# PHASE 12, PART 1: System Design Principles, Estimation, and Distributed Systems

> Interview phase for engineers targeting senior (5-8 yrs), staff (8-12 yrs), and principal
> system design rounds. This part is the *vocabulary and arithmetic* layer: the numbers you must
> produce on a whiteboard without hesitating, and the vocabulary for reasoning about scale.
>
> Difficulty legend used throughout:
> `🟢 Beginner · Must-know` · `🟡 Intermediate · Must-know` · `🔴 Advanced · Must-know` · `⚫ Expert`

---

## 12.1 Scalability Principles

**Section goal.** By the end of 12.1 you should be able to take any architecture diagram and
answer, for each component: *what breaks first, at what load, and what is my migration path off
this decision?*

**The single organizing idea.** Scalability is not "more machines." It is **removing every place
where state forces a single writer, a single bottleneck, or a single human**. Every principle in
this section is a way of moving state out of the critical path — into replicas, into caches, into
the client, into a sharded keyspace, or off the request path entirely.

**The ladder of intervention.** Engineers reach for a distributed data store far too early. Use
this order, and say it out loud in an interview — it is one of the strongest signals you can give:

```
1. Fix the query           (N+1, missing index, bad plan)         ~ free
2. Add a cache             (app cache, then CDN)                    hours
3. Read replicas           (offload reads)                         days
4. Vertical scale          (bigger box / managed autoscale)         days
5. Denormalize             (duplicate the hot join)                 days
6. Connection proxy        (PgBouncer / ProxySQL / pgbouncer)      days
7. Partition / shard       (key-range or hash)                     weeks
8. New storage engine      (KV store, wide-column, search index)   weeks
9. Split the service       (microservice boundary)                 months
```

Most "scale to a billion users" interview answers skip to 7 and are wrong. The winning answer
walks 1→6, explains why they are insufficient, and only then proposes 7.

---

### 12.1.1 Vertical vs horizontal scaling, and the point of no return

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
*Vertical scaling* = make the machine bigger (more CPU, RAM, faster disk, bigger NIC). *Horizontal
scaling* = make more machines and put a router in front.

**Mechanics — vertical.**
Scaling up is a single, trivially correct operation: stop traffic (or not — most databases can
accept connections during a resize), add RAM, start again. There is **no coordination, no
consensus, no data movement, no rebalancing, no new code**. Latency improves for everything at
once because the working set stays in cache and there is no network hop. It is the cheapest
possible form of scale, and for a long time it is the right one.

The ceiling on vertical scaling is physical and economic:

| Dimension | Practical ceiling | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RAM | 512 GB – 2 TB per socket | DIMM slots, memory controllers, NUMA topology |
| CPU cores | 64 – 128 logical | NUMA distance; cross-socket coherency gets expensive |
| Disk (spinning) | ~20 MB/s per spindle | Seek-bound; IOPS ceiling, not capacity ceiling |
| Disk (NVMe) | ~7 GB/s, ~1M–3M IOPS | Hardware protocol ceiling |
| Network | 100 Gb/s ≈ 12.5 GB/s | NIC and PCIe lanes |
| PCIe lanes | ~256 lanes total | Every GPU/NIC/storage controller consumes lanes |

The scaling result is also sublinear because of diminishing cache returns:

- 1 core: ~1x throughput, 1x latency (baseline, e.g. 3 ms)
- 8 cores: ~7x throughput, latency roughly flat
- 64 cores: ~35–45x throughput, latency rises from lock contention and cross-core traffic

**Mechanics — horizontal.**
You add N identical stateless machines behind a load balancer. Each handles ~1/N of traffic.
The costs you now pay:

1. **State must move out of the process.** Sessions to Redis/cookies, uploads to object storage.
2. **Consistency becomes your problem.** Two machines can disagree.
3. **Deploys must be coordinated** so you do not have two versions live.
4. **Observability explodes** — you cannot `top` a fleet.
5. **Operational surface grows superlinearly** — 1 server has 1 failure mode; 500 servers have
   partitions, rolling deploys, version skew, config skew, certificate rotation, DNS lag.

**The point of no return.**
There is a threshold: **when you need more write throughput than one commodity machine can
provide, and your data does not fit in one machine's RAM.** At that moment you must shard.
Once you shard, you have crossed a one-way door:

- Cross-shard transactions become your problem (no joins across shards, no foreign keys).
- Cross-shard queries become your problem (fan-out scatter-gather, or a denormalized index).
- Rebalancing becomes a multi-day online operation, not a 5-minute resize.
- Every future schema change must be rolled out across N shards, often with dual-write.
- Uniqueness constraints become "unique within a shard" plus application-level enforcement.

**Interviewer probes you must be ready for.**
- *"When is vertical scaling actually wrong?"* → When the data working set exceeds single-machine
  RAM (spilling to disk), when write IOPS saturates one device, or when you need >99.99%, which
  requires ≥2 machines for redundancy (a single machine is a SPOF no matter how big).
- *"How do you decide the order of vertical then horizontal?"* → Measure. Vertical until the
  cost curve bends (past ~8–16 cores), or until the instance size you need is not sold / not
  price-efficient. On cloud, compare to horizontally scaling the stateless tier, which is nearly
  free. **Never vertically scale a stateless app tier** — it is cheaper to add more instances.
- *"How do you scale a read-heavy DB vertically, then?"* → Add read replicas (horizontal scaling
  of a specific role) and put replicas in the read path with lag-based staleness guarantees.

**Common mistakes.**
- Treating vertical scaling as a temporary hack you will "grow out of". Sometimes you won't; a
  2 TB RAM machine is a perfectly respectable 10-year architecture.
- Claiming vertical scaling requires downtime. Managed databases resize with little or no
  downtime.
- Assuming vertical scaling is always cheaper. Above ~64 vCPU, per-vCPU price rises, and you pay
  for idle headroom 24/7 while a horizontally scaled fleet can scale to zero at night.
- Scaling the database before finding out that the bottleneck was an N+1 query.

**Production considerations.**
- Measure NUMA effects before committing to very large instances; a 64-core query with
  cross-socket memory access can be *slower* than two 32-core machines.
- Keep a documented "how do we resize this?" runbook per datastore, including how long it takes.
- Understand whether an instance type limits RAM vs vCPU vs IOPS; buying vCPU you cannot feed is
  the most common over-provisioning mistake.

---

### 12.1.2 Load balancing algorithms and health checks

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
A load balancer (LB) is the decision point that maps inbound requests onto backend targets. In
interviews this is often where people say "round-robin" and stop. Know the full menu and, more
importantly, know *when you would move off the default*.

**The algorithm menu.**

| Algorithm | How it picks | Best for | Fails when |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Round robin (RR)** | Next target in a fixed rotation | Homogeneous targets, cheap ops | Any target has variable cost |
| **Weighted RR** | RR with per-target weights | Target sizes differ (t3 vs m5.4xl) | Weights go stale as capacity changes |
| **Least connections** | Target with fewest *active* connections | Long-lived connections, heterogeneous latency | Requests queue rather than error, so "active" misleads |
| **Least requests / least outstanding** | Fewest in-flight requests | Mixed request cost, mixed endpoints | Needs accurate in-flight accounting |
| **Least response time (least latency)** | Lowest EWMA latency | Heterogeneous machines, auto-scaled fleets | Sensitive to a single slow probe |
| **IP hash / consistent hash** | Hash(client IP) mod N | Sticky sessions, per-client affinity | Breaks on every scale event; ignores health |
| **Random (power of two choices)** | Pick 2 at random, send to the less loaded | Huge fleets, stateless services, cheap | No affinity; needs load signal |
| **Weighted locality-aware** | Topology + weights + health | Bare metal, multi-rack, reduce cross-AZ traffic | Complex; needs good topology data |
| **Queue / least-time** | Send to shortest *queue* | Systems that queue rather than reject | Queueing hides overload |

**Which one is the default and why.** In a cloud environment with an ALB/NLB, the default is
usually "least outstanding requests" or "round robin over a *target group* of N instances," where
scaling is handled by the group membership rather than the algorithm. For large fleets of cheap
homogeneous workers (queue consumers, transcoders), **power of two random choices** is genuinely
better than round robin because it needs no shared coordination state and self-corrects.

**Layered LBs — know the layers.**

```
Client
  |
DNS (anycast / geo / weighted / latency-based)      coarse, cached, slow to converge
  |
Global accelerator / edge PoP (Anycast IP)          collapses the routing problem
  |
L4 load balancer (TCP/UDP, connection-level)        10s of millions of conn/s, no TLS termination
  |
L7 load balancer / reverse proxy (HTTP)             host/path routing, TLS termination, caching, WAF
  |
Service mesh sidecar (L7 proxy, per-service)       retries, timeouts, mTLS, circuit breaking
  |
Application
```

Practical guidance:
- **L4** when you need max throughput or long-lived connections (gRPC, websockets, game traffic).
- **L7** when you need routing by path/host/header, TLS termination, compression, or caching.
- **DNS** should never be in the load-balancing hot path for failover; TTLs and client caches mean
  failover takes minutes. Use DNS for geo/weight, not for health.

**Health checks — the mechanics.**

| Check type | What it probes | Cost | Blind to |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **TCP connect** | SYN/SYN-ACK on the port | Very cheap | App is deadlocked but port open |
| **HTTP GET /** | A cheap endpoint returning 200 | Cheap | Deep dependency failures if /healthz is shallow |
| **Readiness** | "Can this instance serve *now*?" | Cheap | Nothing wrong, but drains during deploys |
| **Liveness** | "Is this process wedged and needs a kill?" | Cheap | Over-aggressive liveness causes restart storms |
| **Deep dependency check** | Queries the DB and cache | Expensive, can cascade | Amplifies outages — see below |
| **Passive / outlier detection** | Observed error rate, latency, 5xx | Free | Slow to detect (needs a window) |

**The rule that matters most: separate liveness from readiness from dependency health.**
- `liveness` → answers "restart this process?" Should be dependency-free. If liveness depends on
  the database, a DB blip restarts your entire fleet, which drops every connection, which drops
  more DB load, which makes it worse. This is a real, famous outage shape.
- `readiness` → answers "route traffic here?" This may include dependencies, and failing it is the
  right response during a dependency outage.
- `startup` → protects slow-booting processes from liveness restarts.

**Outlier detection.** Modern balancers (Envoy, Linkerd, cloud LBs) continuously compute per-endpoint
success rate and latency, and *eject* an endpoint from the load-balancing pool when it is a
statistical outlier (e.g. 5 consecutive errors, or >3σ from the fleet median). This is strictly
better than active health checks for detecting a *partially* broken instance — the one that accepts
TCP but fails 1% of requests. **This is a strong thing to mention in an interview**: "I'd enable
outlier detection so that a single partially-degraded pod gets ejected rather than sending 1% of
traffic into a void."

**When to use vs not.**
- Use L7 + path routing when you have multiple services in one process (monolith) and want to
  extract one without a deploy.
- Use L4 + connection-level balancing when requests are long-lived or the protocol is not HTTP.
- Do **not** add sticky sessions to hide a statefulness bug. Sticky sessions destroy the ability
  to replace a failed instance and make rolling deploys impossible. If you need affinity, use it
  at the *data* layer (shard by user ID) not at the connection layer.
- Do **not** balance to the *slowest* thing. Balanced round robin can create synchronized bursts
  across backends (thundering herd at the tick boundary); jitter or power-of-two avoids it.

**Trade-offs.**
- Consistent-hash/sticky balancing: perfect affinity, terrible failover and no rebalancing.
- Least-connections: better for slow requests, requires per-connection tracking (state in the LB).
- Active health checks: fast and simple, but they can *themselves* be the load that takes down the
  system during an incident, and they cannot detect slow-but-alive.
- Weighted algorithms: powerful but require a config pipeline; a wrong weight is a silent capacity
  bug that nobody notices until latency rises.

**Common mistakes.**
- Health check endpoint that touches the database, wired to liveness.
- Health check interval of 1 second across 500 pods → 500 QPS of load against the DB just to say
  "hello."
- Assuming the LB removes the need for retries. It removes the need for *load balancing* retries,
  not for application-level retries on transient errors.
- Forgetting to drain connections: `SIGTERM` → stop accepting new → wait for in-flight →
  deregister from LB → then exit. Getting this wrong makes every deploy an outage.
- Using DNS round-robin for load balancing. Your clients cache it for the entire TTL and you have
  lost all control.
- Not distinguishing \"target group health\" (LB's view) from \"service ready\" (app's view).

**Interviewer probes.**
- *\"Your LB is sending 20% of requests to one bad node. How do you find out?\"* → per-endpoint
  request/error/latency metrics (Envoy stats, ALB access logs), then outlier detection / passive
  ejection, then a health-check fix.
- *\"How do you do zero-downtime deploys?\"* → readiness false → drain → deregister → wait
  max_in_flight + keepalive → terminate; LB connection draining; expand-contract for schema.
- *\"Should the LB retry?\"* → Only for safe, idempotent requests, and only if it surfaces the
  retry to your metrics. Blind retries on non-idempotent POSTs create double charges.

**Production considerations.**
- Track `lb_target_concurrent_requests` / in-flight as a saturation metric, not CPU.
- Keep max connections per target well above your typical concurrent load; a target that hits its
  connection ceiling queues and looks healthy.
- Use slow-start on newly registered targets (Envoy/LB7 `slow_start`) so a batch of new pods does
  not receive 1/N of traffic before their JIT/cache is warm.
- Anycast + a global LB collapses the routing problem to one IP and removes BGP-based failover
  complexity; the cost is loss of region pinning.

### 12.1.3 Stateless servers: why session state is the enemy

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
A *stateless service* keeps no request-specific state in the process. Given the same request and
the same externally visible state, any instance can produce the same response. This is not a
performance optimization — it is the **precondition for horizontal scaling, rolling deploys,
autoscaling, and reliable failover**.

**The three places state hides.**

1. **In-process memory** — the obvious one. `Map<sessionId, User>` in a servlet, a module-level
   dict, a `@Cacheable` local map.
2. **In the local filesystem** — uploads to `/tmp`, SQLite files, generated PDFs, log buffers.
   A pod restart loses them; a scaled-out pod does not have them.
3. **In implicit process identity** — "the item currently being processed by *this* worker," an
   in-memory rate limiter, an idempotency cache that dies with the process.

**Mechanics — moving state out.**

| Original | Replacement | Cost | Caveat |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| In-process session map | Signed/encrypted cookie | Zero added latency, no network | Cookie ≤4 KB; can't revoke instantly |
| In-process session map | Redis / DynamoDB session store | ~1 ms per request | Adds a dependency on the hot path |
| JVM heap cache (Caffeine) | Bounded local cache | Still per-instance | Caches *approximations*; TTL short (5–60 s) |
| Local file upload | S3/Blob + presigned URL | Object store is the source of truth | Async finalize; two-phase visibility |
| Local rate limiter | Token bucket in Redis / edge | Atomic increments needed | Redis becomes a dependency; use a lease |
| Local feature flag cache | Config service + push | Fast | Push invalidation must be reliable |
| Local dedupe cache | Idempotency key store in DB | Correct | Must be in the same transaction |

**The cookie-session pattern, done right.**
The server *signs* (HMAC-SHA256, with a key from a KMS) a small payload: `{userId, role, iat,
exp}`. The client returns it on every request. There is no lookup, so the request path has **no
dependency on the session store**. The trade-offs are explicit and you should name them:
- You cannot log the user out everywhere (no server-side revocation) → mitigate with a short TTL
  plus a denylist of revoked session IDs / a `session_version` column checked only on sensitive ops.
- Data is visible (encrypt, or accept visibility) and tamper-evident (sign).
- 4 KB hard limit → store only identity, never profile blobs.

**The "not stateless but honest" pattern: sticky sessions at the data layer.**
If state is expensive to externalize, choose your *shard key* to be the same key as your routing
key. Route by `hash(userId)` to both the app instance and the DB shard, and the app is effectively
stateless *for that workload*. This is what single-tenant SaaS and many games do.

**When to use stateless vs not.**
- Use stateless for: request/response services, public content APIs, anything you want to autoscale
  or deploy 100× a day.
- Keep state in-process when: it is genuinely ephemeral and recomputable (a compiled regex, an
  LRU of parsed templates), or when the service is a singleton by design (a leader, an index
  builder, a stream processor with a partition assignment).

**Trade-offs.**
- Externalized session stores add latency (~0.5–1 ms) and a new failure mode (Redis down = logins
  break). Mitigation: cache sessions locally with a short TTL, and make the store a *write-through*
  only at login.
- Local caches give you speed and staleness. The correct mental model: **a local cache is fine for
  anything a user would call "slightly wrong", and forbidden for anything they would call "lost
  money" or "wrong permission."**
- Statelessness makes the *database* the bottleneck faster. That is a feature: it forces you to
  confront the real constraint instead of hiding it behind an in-process cache.

**Production considerations.**
- Instance metadata is a trap: "is my primary DB host A or B?" or "which shard am I serving?"
  is state. Use a config service / gossip / a sharding library.
- Warm-up: a freshly started pod has a cold cache and a cold JIT. Use slow-start and pre-warming
  so autoscaling does not create a latency cliff.
- Rolling deploy safety: your code must tolerate 99% old version + 1% new version during any
  window. That means backward-compatible APIs and expand-contract schema migrations.

**Common mistakes.**
- "It's stateless because we use a JWT" — the *service* is stateless but the JWT is a bearer
  token; if it contains permissions, permission revocation takes TTL time. Say that out loud.
- Storing a growing list (permissions, entitlements) in the cookie. Now every request is bigger
  and you are near the 4 KB cliff.
- Taking a dependency on an in-process rate limiter and assuming it is global.
- Testing failover by killing the pod and being surprised that in-flight uploads vanished.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How would you make this service stateless?"* → Inventory state → classify (identity / cache /
  durable) → cookies for identity, bounded TTL caches for cache, DB+idempotency for durable.
- *"What breaks during a rolling deploy if you're not stateless?"* → Session affinity pins users to
  old pods, so deploys take as long as the session timeout; a pod crash drops sessions.
- *"Is a service with a local LRU cache stateless?"* → Strictly no; practically yes if the cache is
  a pure function of external state. State your assumption and the invalidation source.

---

### 12.1.4 CDNs: what they cache, what they break, cache invalidation at scale

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
A CDN (content delivery network) is a geographically distributed set of caches plus a router that
sends each user to a nearby POP. It buys you three things: **lower latency** (RTT to user instead
of RTT to origin), **origin offload** (most requests never reach your servers), and **DDoS
absorption** (volumetric floods die at the edge).

**The cache key and the two-request model.**

```
Request → Edge POP
   ├── cache HIT  → serve from POP (0–20 ms to user)
   └── cache MISS → forward to origin → cache the response → serve
                        └── often via "shield" POP so one miss ≠ N origin hits
```

The two-request model is the key operational fact: **a miss costs 2× RTT and exposes your
origin**. A single un-cacheable hot object can therefore be a self-inflicted DDoS.

**What a CDN can and cannot cache.**

| Cacheable by default | Not cacheable by default | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Static assets: js/css/img/font/wasm | `Set-Cookie` responses | Cookies often disable caching entirely |
| Public HTML with explicit TTL | Authenticated HTML | Use signed URLs / cookies with `private` |
| Public API GET/HEAD responses | Anything with `Authorization` | Restrict via config, not hope |
| Public image variants (AVIF/WebP) | `Vary`-heavy responses | `Vary: *` effectively disables caching |
| HLS/DASH segments | Websocket upgrade | Manifests are cacheable; segments are content-addressed |
| Public error pages | Personalized 4xx | Cache 404s briefly to absorb scrapers |

**The invalidation problem.** Two primitive strategies, and everything else is a mix:

1. **TTL (time-to-live)** — `Cache-Control: max-age=300, s-maxage=86400`. Simple, eventually
   consistent, bounds staleness. This is your default.
2. **Purge / explicit invalidation** — tell the CDN "drop key K". Fast but has costs and races.

Purge mechanics you should know:
- **Surrogate key / tag purge** — tag objects (`/users/42/avatar`), purge by tag. This is the only
  purge design that scales; a per-URL purge API will melt under fan-out.
- **Soft purge (mark stale)** — serve stale while revalidating in the background. Users never see
  an error; you get eventual freshness.
- **Hard purge** — evict immediately, forcing a miss storm at the origin. Protect it with a rate
  limit and a shield POP.
- **Versioned URLs** — `/app.4f2c1a.js` with `immutable, max-age=31536000`. Content-addressed
  URLs make invalidation *unnecessary*: when content changes, the URL changes. **This is the
  correct answer to "how do you do cache invalidation" in most interviews**, and saying it earns
  points.
- **Stale-while-revalidate / stale-if-error** — the two most valuable directives:
  `stale-while-revalidate=60` (serve stale, refresh in background) and `stale-if-error=86400`
  (serve stale if origin is broken). These turn a cache into an availability mechanism.

**The correctness gotchas that break things.**
- **Cache key vs cache content**: `Vary: Accept-Encoding`, `Vary: Accept-Language`, and
  `Vary: Cookie` multiply your hit rate downward geometrically. Each variant is a separate object.
- **Cookie-set responses**: a CDN will not cache anything that sets a cookie unless you force it.
  This is why "we added a CDN and the hit rate is 3%" happens.
- **Range requests and partial content**: byte-range responses are cacheable but complicate purge.
- **Query-string parameters**: many CDNs ignore unknown params in the key, serving A's response to
  B's request. Confirm key composition explicitly.
- **Shared cache + personal data**: a CDN in front of authenticated HTML with a permissive
  `s-maxage` is a data-leak incident waiting to happen. Use `private`, or split the domain so the
  CDN cannot serve the authenticated surface at all. **Splitting authenticated and public content
  onto different domains/CDN distributions is a design worth advocating.**

**Signed requests (secure CDN).** Rather than caching per-user HTML, expose content through
token-authenticated URLs: `?exp=...&sig=HMAC(path+exp, secret)`. The edge validates the signature in
microseconds and caches the object. This gives you CDN offload for personalized content without
caching per-user variants. Trade-offs: clock skew tolerance, secret rotation, and cache-key
staleness on token rotation.

**Tiered caching.** POP → regional mid-tier (shield) → origin. Shields absorb misses so that
N simultaneous misses for the same object become 1 origin fetch. Pair with a **cache-collaboration
** feature (AWS CloudFront, Fastly) where POPs share cache fills, which turns a global cold miss
into a regional fill.

**When to use vs not.**
- Use: read-heavy public content, media, software distribution, static site generation output.
- Be careful: authenticated HTML, anything personalized, anything where a 60-second staleness
  window changes a business outcome (price! inventory! ad targeting!).
- Do not use a CDN to hide a missing HTTP cache layer. If your origin still gets 80% of requests,
  your `Cache-Control` headers are wrong.

**Trade-offs.**
- Caching moves the failure domain: now origin health checks matter less but cache poisoning and
  purge-amplification attacks matter more.
- Purge APIs cost money per operation and have latency; high-churn content should use short TTLs
  or versioned URLs rather than purges.
- A POP cache is also a **privacy boundary**: anyone at the edge operator can read what you cache.

**Common mistakes.**
- Caching an API response and forgetting that the payload contains another user's data.
- Purge-per-write in a high-write system (this is a self-inflicted availability event).
- Long `max-age` with no versioning → users stuck on a broken JS bundle for a year. Pair long TTLs
  with `immutable` and content hashes, and keep an emergency "kill switch" URL rewrite.
- Not measuring **hit ratio per POP and per object class**, so you optimize the wrong objects.
- Forgetting that a CDN adds a hop to your TLS handshake and TTFB budget; measure end-to-end.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Your deploy ships a broken JS file. 40 million users have it cached. What do you do?"* →
  versioned assets so the new HTML references a new URL; ship a kill-switch flag; purge by tag as
  the fallback; the cache TTL is your blast radius, so keep HTML TTL short (60–300 s).
- *"How would you use a CDN for a personalized feed?"* → CDN the shell (HTML/JS/CSS, short TTL),
  API the data with signed requests or no caching, and consider edge-side includes for the
  truly cacheable fragments.
- *"What is the biggest CDN risk?"* → stale or wrongly-keyed content served globally at speed,
  i.e. a correctness bug amplified by a million.

### 12.1.5 Database scaling: read replicas, connection proxies, caching, sharding, partitioning

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
Database scaling is the art of increasing the throughput, latency, or capacity of a datastore
without changing the shape of your data model more than necessary. There are exactly five tools,
and you should be able to name all five plus their failure modes on demand.

**The five tools, in order of cost.**

```
1. Cache           (Redis / in-process / CDN)         hides reads
2. Read replicas   (async copies)                     multiplies read capacity
3. Connection proxy (PgBouncer / ProxySQL)            multiplies concurrency
4. Partition       (range/time)                       prunes data
5. Shard           (hash)                             multiplies writes + capacity
```

**1. Caching.**

Patterns:
- **Cache-aside (lazy loading)** — app reads cache; on miss reads DB, writes cache with TTL. The
  default. Risk: stampede.
- **Read-through** — the cache itself fetches from the DB. Cleaner call sites, no double logic.
- **Write-through / write-behind (async)** — writes go to cache first; write-behind also writes DB
  later. Fast but **you can lose writes** if the process dies between.
- **Write-around** — write to DB, invalidate cache. Avoids filling cache with data nobody reads.

Three problems to know by name:
- **Cache stampede / thundering herd** — 10,000 requests miss the same key at once. Fixes:
  single-flight/locking (one request loads, others wait), probabilistic early expiration
  (`exp - 30s * rand()%1000 < now`), request coalescing at the CDN, or a short negative-cache TTL.
- **Cache penetration** — requests for keys that never exist (someone enumerating IDs). Fix:
  cache the *negative* result, bloom filter, or validate the key format.
- **Cache avalanche** — a whole layer of cache dies or a large keyspace expires together. Fix:
  jittered TTLs (`max_age * (0.9 + 0.2*rand())`), replicas, and a warm-up on restart.

**2. Read replicas.**

Mechanics: the primary writes to its WAL and streams changes to replicas asynchronously.
Replica count multiplies read capacity roughly linearly for read-only workloads, and none of the
data is duplicated on disk (it's a full copy, but on separate machines).

| Property | Value | Consequence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Replication lag | 0 – 2 s typically; seconds under load; can be minutes during failover | Read-your-writes breaks unless you pin |
| Read capacity | ~1× per replica | Great for read-heavy; useless for write-heavy |
| Cost | Full copy of storage, per replica | Storage cost dominates at high ratios |
| Failure behavior | If primary dies, replica may lose acknowledged writes | RPO > 0 without sync replication |

Techniques you must know:
- **Read-your-writes**: after a write, route reads for that key to the primary for a short window,
  or use a replica in the *same region/AZ* as the primary, or pass a `min_version` and have the
  replica wait until it has applied it (PostgreSQL `recovery_min_apply_lsn`/`pg_sync_replication`).
- **Monotonic reads**: pin a client to the same replica for a bounded window to prevent
  time-going-backwards.
- **Synchronous replication** (semi-synchronous / quorum) trades RPO=0 for write latency: you wait
  for 1 or 2 replicas to acknowledge.
- **Read splitting is where correctness bugs live.** The classic bug: user updates their profile,
  the next GET goes to a replica and shows the old value. Always name this risk when you propose
  replicas.

**3. Connection proxies.**
The most under-appreciated scaling tool. Postgres defaults to `max_connections=100` and each
connection is an OS process (~5–10 MB). 1,000 app instances × 20 connections = 20,000 connections
→ 20,000 backend processes → memory exhaustion, context-switch thrash, scheduler collapse.

- **PgBouncer in transaction mode** — multiplexes thousands of client connections onto a few dozen
  server connections. Requires **no session-level state**: no `LISTEN/NOTIFY`, no `SET` without
  reset, no advisory locks held across transactions, no temp tables across transactions.
- **ProxySQL / MaxScale** — query-aware: can route by SQL text, cache, mirror, and split by
  read/write.
- **RDS Proxy / cloud equivalents** — managed, with automatic pooling and failover-aware routing.

Rule of thumb: Postgres connection targets are best kept around **2–4× cores**, not "as many as
possible." Always put a proxy in front of Postgres in a scaled architecture and say the number.

**4. Partitioning.**
Splitting one table into many *physical* tables inside one database. It prunes I/O and enables
partition-wise operations; it does **not** increase write throughput beyond the single instance
(it can actually hurt, due to planning overhead and more open files).

- **RANGE partitioning by time** — the workhorse. `ORDER BY created_at` scans only the partitions
  you ask for; `DROP PARTITION` for retention is instant (`DELETE` of 2 billion rows is not).
  Downside: skew/hot partitions, and if you always query "all time" you get no pruning.
- **LIST / enum partitioning** — by country, by tenant tier, by status. Often combined with range.
- **HASH partitioning** — spread writes within one instance. Good for very large tables with
  uniform access; hurts range scans, and rebalancing requires DDL.
- **KEY partitioning** — hash on a chosen key.

Key production rules: always partition by a column present in your query predicates; put a
sensible default partition; monitor per-partition size skew; and use `ATTACH/DETACH PARTITION`
online, never `DELETE`.

**5. Sharding.**
Splitting data across *multiple database instances*. Now you have a distributed system, and every
problem from 12.3 applies.

| Strategy | Layout | Pros | Cons |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Hash (mod N)** | `shard = hash(key) % N` | Even distribution, even load | Resharding = full data move; hotspot on hot keys |
| **Consistent hash** | Ring with virtual nodes | Minimal movement on change | Uneven load, needs vnodes |
| **Range / directory** | Ranges of keyspace, or a lookup table | Range scans work, easy to reason | Hotspots at range boundaries; needs a directory service |
| **Tenant / account** | One tenant → one shard | Isolation, per-tenant backup/restore | Big tenants become hot shards |
| **Geo** | User → home region | Latency, data residency | Cross-region access gets slow; migrations needed |

The **directory / lookup-table** approach (a `shard_map` table or a consistent-hash ring stored
in ZooKeeper/etcd/Redis) is the most operationally mature: you can move ranges without rehashing
everything, and you can pin a specific key to a specific shard.

**Uniqueness across shards.** A `UNIQUE(email)` constraint becomes `UNIQUE(email) ` *per shard*.
Global uniqueness requires either a separate global index (another sharded table with the same
key, or a global uniqueness service) or accepting eventual duplicates and deduplicating later.
Name this in an interview — it is a detail most candidates miss and interviewers notice.

**Cross-shard queries.** Options, in order of preference:
1. Denormalize so the query never crosses (write a copy into the other shard, or a read model).
2. Scatter-gather in the app (parallel fan-out + merge), with a strict fan-out cap.
3. Maintain a global secondary index (ES, or a dedicated index shard).
4. A separate OLAP store synced from CDC.

**Rebalancing.** The hard part. Approaches:
- **Hash-based rebalance** — change the ring, move only the affected ranges. Live with brief
  double-writes or a brief read-repair window.
- **Split a hot shard into two, then rebalance** — this is what Uber-style sharding tools do
  (`ShardKeeper`): shards are *physical* databases containing many *logical* shards, so you can
  move a logical shard (with its data) to another physical node.
- **Dual-write + backfill + cutover + verify** — the standard online migration recipe.

**When to use vs not.**
- Not: below ~50–100 GB of data, below ~10k writes/sec, or if your access pattern is unknown.
  Most systems never need sharding; they needed an index.
- Yes: single-writer write throughput ceiling reached, data no longer fits comfortably on one
  machine, or you need write scale by tenant.

**Trade-offs summary.**
Caching trades correctness for speed. Replicas trade consistency for read scale. Partitioning
trades planning simplicity for I/O pruning and free retention. Sharding trades operational sanity
for write scale. Every one of these is a one-way-ish door; the interview skill is knowing *which
door you are opening and why*.

**Common mistakes.**
- Sharding before measuring. Run `pg_stat_statements`, find the top query, and fix it.
- Choosing a shard key that does not co-locate the query. Shard by the key you *read* by, and by
  the entity that dominates the access pattern, not by a value that merely looks "even."
- Ignoring hotspots: with 1M users and hash sharding, one user with 5,000 followers generates a
  hot shard. Detect with per-shard key-count and QPS metrics.
- Believing read replicas are free. A read replica is a full storage copy and can serve as a
  failover target (and then you must handle split-brain and lost writes).
- Not having a plan for schema change across shards (dual-write, backfill, verify, cutover).

**Production considerations.**
- Metrics per shard: QPS, p99 latency, rows, storage bytes, replication lag, and a *balance*
  metric (max/median shard size). Alert on imbalance, not on absolute values.
- Make the shard count and the hash function *parameters*, not code. Config-driven sharding is the
  difference between a 1-week and a 6-month rebalancing project.
- Test with a **chaos experiment**: kill a primary, add 100 ms of latency, partition a shard.
  If you have never done it, you have never done it.
- Automate shard lifecycle: provision, rebalance, snapshot, restore, decommission.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How many shards should I create?"* → Start at a modest number sized for ~2–3 years of growth
  (e.g. 8–32) and make it splittable, because the count should be a parameter. Optionally
  pre-shard by a factor of 16 with a directory map so early shards are just empty.
- *"A hotspot appears on one shard. What now?"* → Identify the hot key (per-key QPS), then:
  split the keyspace for that hot tenant into its own shards (a "tenant escape hatch"), add a
  per-key replica fan-out, or cache the hot read path.
- *"Read replica lag spiked to 30 seconds during failover. Now what?"* → The new primary is
  replaying WAL; users see stale reads. Mitigate by (a) read-your-writes pinning, (b) surfacing
  staleness to callers, (c) promoting a replica in a region that is already caught up.

---

### 12.1.6 Consistent hashing and virtual nodes

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
Consistent hashing maps keys to nodes on a ring by hash, so that adding or removing a node
requires moving only `1/N` of the keys (instead of rehashing everything, which naive modulo does).
**Virtual nodes (vnodes)** fix consistent hashing's real problem — load imbalance.

**The classic modulo problem (naive).**
With `shard = hash(key) % N`, going from N=8 to N=9 remaps essentially **every key** (only keys
where `hash % 9 == hash % 8` stay put, about 1/9 of them). Full data migration, offline, for
every resize. Unacceptable.

**The consistent hash ring: mechanics.**

```
1. Hash each node (or each vnode) with the SAME hash function as keys → point on ring [0, 2^32).
2. Sort all node points around the ring.
3. For a key k: h = hash(k); walk clockwise to the first node point; that node owns k.
4. Add node: only keys that hash between the new node's point and its successor move.  ≈ 1/N.
5. Remove node: only keys in that node's arc move.                            ≈ 1/N.
6. Virtual nodes: each physical node gets V points (V = 128–512 typical), so its share is
   ~V/(V*N) of the ring and the variance collapses.
```

**Why vnodes matter, concretely.** With 3 nodes and no vnodes, the ring arcs are uniform random
variables; the probability of a reasonable split is low, and you can easily end up with an arc
covering 70% of the ring. With V=160 vnodes per node, each node owns 160 arcs, so the per-node
share concentrates around 1/N with tiny variance. **Rule: use at least 100 vnodes per node, and
make V a parameter so you can change the ratio without re-adding all nodes.**

**Ranged consistent hashing (Dynamo-style, and what you should actually use).**
Dynamo uses *ranges of the token ring assigned to nodes*, not single keys. Consequences you should
be able to articulate:
- **Range replicas** (N, W, R) are natural — see 12.3.9.
- **Parallelism**: one node holds a contiguous range, so a range scan is a sequential read.
- **Hotspot avoidance**: you can split exactly the hot range.
- **Bootstrap cost**: assigning contiguous ranges requires walking keys, so Dynamo *does* sample
  the keyspace from a random node to build the range map (a real, interviewable detail).

**Rebalancing on top of the ring: three mechanics.**
1. **Pending ranges** — mark a range as moving; the source stops serving it and the destination
   catches up from a replica/snapshot, then takes over.
2. **Replica priority (Dynamo's approach)** — when a node rejoins, give it the ranges where it is
   the *highest-priority* replica, which is always safe.
3. **Hinted handoff** — if the target node is down, a temporary neighbor accepts the write and
   records a hint; when the node returns, it replays the hints. This keeps availability > consistency.
4. **Read repair** — on a read, if a replica has stale data, the coordinator writes the newest
   value back to it. (Often paired with a Merkle-tree anti-entropy sweep to find divergence
   cheaply.)

**Data structures you must be able to sketch.**
- **R-tree / bounding box tree** — on-disk, clustered-by-location, for range scans.
- **LSM tree** — memtable (sorted in RAM) + SSTable on disk + compaction. Great write throughput,
  reads may need multiple SSTables; used by Cassandra, RocksDB, LevelDB.
- **B+ tree** — the classic RDBMS index. Great range scans and read locality; random writes cost a
  page split + fsync.
- **Skip list / concurrent skip list** — in-memory, ordered, log(N) search, lock-free-ish inserts.

**Consistent hashing beyond sharding: cache and cluster membership.**
The same ring places cache nodes so that a key maps to the same node for the cache layer, and
cluster membership services (etcd, Consul, Chef) use it to distribute configuration. "Where does
this key live" is the same question in all three.

**Consistent hashing vs rendezvous hashing (HRW).** Rendezvous hashing computes, for each key,
`score(key, node) = hash(key || node)` and picks the maximum. Properties: **no ring to maintain,
no virtual nodes, and adding/removing a node moves exactly the same minimal set of keys** — and
it's provably minimal per-key. It costs O(N) hashes per lookup instead of O(log N), so cache the
ring and use rendezvous only when you care about perfect rebalance properties. HRW with weighted
nodes gives proportional allocation cleanly. This is a strong, differentiating answer.

**When to use vs not.**
- Use when: nodes join/leave frequently, data must move a minimal amount, and you need stable key
  → node mapping for caching.
- Do not use when: the node set is small and mostly static (a 4-node cache — just use modulo with a
  prime and a config reload), or when you need complex queries that a range store handles better.

**Trade-offs.**
- Ring management is itself a distributed problem: all nodes must agree on the ring. Solutions:
  store the ring in a strongly-consistent store (etcd/zk/Redis), gossip the ring with version
  numbers (Riak, Cassandra), or compute it deterministically from a config file everyone can read.
- Deterministic-from-config rings are dramatically simpler to operate and are the right answer in
  an interview: "the ring is derived from a versioned config; nodes are stateless with respect to
  the ring; ring changes are deploys."
- Churn during rebalance: brief windows of partial availability or double ownership. Handle with
  pending states + hinted handoff, or accept it and design for read repair.

**Common mistakes.**
- Modulo with a composite shard count (`hash % 64 % 16`) — correctness depends on both counts being
  co-prime and is destroyed by any change.
- Too few vnodes, or vnodes chosen randomly per node with a different seed (the ring must be
  computable identically by everyone — same hash function, same node IDs, same vnode indices).
- Assuming consistent hashing gives *perfect* balance. It gives *expected* balance; hot keys still
  need splitting.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"You're using `hash(key) % 1024` and it works. Why change?"* → When you need to add shards
  without a full migration, or to place replicas with correlated failure domains (racks/AZs) so
  replicas are far apart on the ring.
- *"Walk me through adding a node to your ring."* → publish ring version → compute new node's arc
  → source node streams that range (or a snapshot) → verify checksums → flip ownership → old
  owner deletes → update clients of the new ring version.
- *"Consistent hash or rendezvous hash?"* → Rendezvous for simplicity and optimality when N is
  small enough to hash all candidates; consistent hash (with vnodes) when N is large or you need
  incremental ring dissemination.

### 12.1.7 Microservices trade-offs: operational cost as a first-class concern

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
Microservices = independently deployable services aligned to business capabilities, each owning
its data. The benefit is **organizational** (independent teams, independent deploy cadence),
not technical. Most of the value evaporates unless you have multiple teams that can genuinely
deploy independently.

**The full cost ledger — memorize this, it is the most impressive thing you can recite.**

*What you gain:*
- Independent deploy cadence; a change to billing does not require redeploying search.
- Technology heterogeneity per service (choose the right store per problem).
- Fault isolation: a runaway search query does not OOM the orders service.
- Team autonomy and ownership clarity.
- Clear data ownership → each team owns its tables, so schema decisions stay local.

*What you pay:*
- **Network calls replace in-process calls.** Every boundary is a place to fail, be slow, and be
  partially unavailable. You lose type safety across boundaries and must version your APIs.
- **No ACID across services.** You must design explicit compensation (see 12.3.6/12.3.7).
- **Distributed debugging.** Distributed tracing becomes mandatory. The *same* stack trace now
  spans 6 processes and 2,000 ms.
- **Test environments are the hard part.** You cannot clone production data freely; contract
  tests, mocks and a "production-shaped" staging environment all cost engineering time.
- **CI/CD and platform engineering.** 40 services × 5 deploys/day = 200 deploys/day; each needs
  canaries, automated rollback, progressive delivery, and a way to know which version is live.
- **On-call surface.** More services = more pages. You need a real SRE/on-call function, not a
  rotation of exhausted engineers.
- **Service discovery, load balancing, config, secrets, feature flags** all become infrastructure
  you build or buy.
- **Data duplication across services.** User data lives in auth, orders, and recommendations.
  Every service needs a copy → you have an integration/consistency problem.
- **Cost.** Per-service baseline overhead (a service with 5 QPS still costs ~2 vCPU of JVM/HTTP
  overhead). "40 microservices × 3 nodes × $X/mo" is a real line item.

**The honest interview answer.** *Microservices trade a technical problem you understand (one
big codebase, one deploy) for a set of organizational problems (partial failure, data consistency,
operational overhead) that most teams underestimate.* **Start with a well-modularized monolith and
extract services along the seams where change and scale actually differ** — e.g. media processing,
search indexing, notifications, billing. Extract where there is (a) a clear data owner, (b)
independent scaling needs, or (c) an independent team. Do not extract because "it's best practice."

**When to use vs not.**

| Use microservices when | Stay monolithic when |
| --- | --- |
| ≥3 teams owning disjoint domains | 1–2 teams total |
| Domains change at very different rates | Release cadence is acceptable |
| Components need very different scaling profiles | Uniform scaling is fine |
| Fault isolation is a hard requirement (payments vs search) | Latency budget is tight and chatty |
| Compliance boundaries require separate deployment | Team lacks distributed-systems experience |

**Trade-offs that surprise people.**
- Chatty synchronous call chains are the #1 failure cause in microservices. Budget at most 2–3
  synchronous hops on the user-facing path; beyond that, go async (queue) or denormalize.
- **Shared-database-between-services is not microservices** — it's a distributed monolith with
  extra network hops. If service A reads service B's tables directly, you have the worst of both.
- Extraction is not a one-time event; every extraction is a distributed transaction (dual-write +
  backfill + cutover, or change data capture).
- Microservices do not *automatically* increase reliability; they increase the number of independent
  failure causes while giving you the tools (circuit breakers, bulkheads, independent deploys) to
  manage them. Net reliability is a design outcome, not a consequence.

**Production considerations.**
- Service granularity rule: **split when the terms of change or scaling differ**, not by noun.
  A "user service" that changes every hour with "order service" that changes every month is a good
  boundary; splitting "user" into "user-read" and "user-write" services is not.
- Own your telemetry: RED metrics + USE metrics + distributed tracing with consistent trace IDs
  propagated through every hop and every queue message.
- Backward-compatible API evolution is mandatory. Never remove or rename a field; add, deprecate,
  observe usage, then remove.
- Avoid chatty startup dependencies: fail fast, degrade gracefully, serve cached data.
- Set per-service SLOs and an error budget policy so a service can't spend its reliability budget
  silently.

**Common mistakes.**
- Splitting by technical layer (API service / business logic service / data service) instead of by
  domain. This produces the "shared kernel with extra latency."
- Distributed monolith: 12 services, all synchronous, all sharing one schema.
- No versioning strategy → a coordinated deploy of 12 services is needed for every change.
- Splitting before you have automated deploys. Without CI/CD, microservices actively *reduce*
  velocity.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"When would you NOT use microservices?"* → "When there's one team, or when the domain boundary
  isn't stable yet — I'd start modular and extract where scaling or change rate justifies the
  cost."
- *"How do you avoid the distributed monolith?"* → Async messaging for non-request-response work,
  strict latency budgets per hop, no shared schemas, and per-service data ownership enforced by
  credentials (each service gets DB credentials only for its own tables).

---

### 12.1.8 Auto-scaling: reactive vs predictive, and the metrics that matter

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
Autoscaling automatically adjusts the number (or size) of running instances to match load. It is
the mechanism that lets you run a stateless fleet at low average cost with high peak headroom —
and it is also a machine for *creating* incidents if configured naively.

**Scale out vs scale up.**

| | Scale out (horizontal) | Scale up (vertical) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Granularity | 1 instance at a time | One instance type jump |
| Rollback | Drop instances | Must resize back |
| Cold start | Yes (image pull, JIT warmup) | Usually restart |
| Cost profile | Matches load, scales to zero | Pay for headroom always |
| Good for | Stateless, bursty, spiky traffic | Stateful, single-writer, warm caches |

**Scaling policies.**

- **Target tracking** (recommended default): "keep average CPU at 60%." Simplest, robust.
  Also `requests per second per instance`, `average concurrent requests`, or
  `KEDA`-style custom metrics from a queue backlog.
- **Step scaling**: breach CPU >80% → add 10 instances. Coarse; can overshoot.
- **Scheduled / time-based**: pre-scale for a known 9 AM traffic spike or a game launch. Best
  practice for known patterns — costs nothing when not needed.
- **Predictive scaling**: ML forecasts from historical metrics (daily/weekly seasonality) and
  pre-warms capacity before the forecast peak. Strong on weekday-corporate traffic, weak on
  truly random or one-off spikes.
- **KEDA / queue-depth scaling**: scale on the *work backlog*, not on CPU. For async systems this
  is almost always the right metric, because CPU is a lagging proxy for queue length.
- **HPA cooldown windows** — too short = flapping (scale up fast, scale down slowly).

**The metric that matters, per workload.** This is a favorite probe.

| Workload | Scale on | Don't scale on |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Stateless HTTP API | RPS per instance, or CPU | Memory alone (GC noise) |
| Queue workers | **Backlog count / oldest message age** | CPU |
| WebSocket servers | Active connections | CPU |
| Latency-sensitive API | **Concurrency / in-flight requests** | CPU (CPU stays flat as queues grow) |
| ML inference | GPU utilization, queue depth | CPU |
| Batch jobs | Only run when scheduled (scale to zero) | anything reactive |
| Database | Manual, conservative, or connection count | CPU (CPU is meaningless under IO wait) |

**Reactive vs predictive — the real tradeoff.**

Reactive (metric-thresholded autoscale):
- Pros: no forecast model to get wrong; self-corrects; simple; works on novel traffic.
- Cons: reacts *after* the spike starts (misses the first minutes of peak = 5xx), scale-up lags
  image pull + JIT warmup + LB slow start, and scale-down thrashes on noisy metrics.

Predictive (scheduled/forecast-based):
- Pros: capacity is ready *before* the spike; no cold-start penalty; cheaper because you're not
  running oversized peak capacity constantly.
- Cons: a bad forecast either wastes money or under-provisions; needs clean historical data; you
  must **still keep reactive autoscaling** as a safety net.

The interview-worthy synthesis: **use predictive/scheduled scaling to eliminate the cold-start
penalty on known peaks, and reactive scaling as the backstop for the unknown.** Many teams do
both, exactly this way.

**Production considerations / how to not have a scaling incident.**
- Warm-up is the #1 cause of autoscaling latency cliffs: image pull (10s), app boot (2s), JIT
  warmup (30–90s for a JVM), cache fill, TLS handshake. Mitigate with pre-baked AMIs, snapshot /
  restore warm pools, JVM AppCDS, connection pre-warming, and LB slow start.
- Always leave headroom: if 1 instance handles 500 RPS and you scale at 70% utilization, you have
  30% to absorb the lag.
- Scale-down needs a drain grace period ≥ longest request + keepalive.
- Instance metadata is state (see 12.1.3) — don't let instances "own" a specific shard's identity
  by IP.
- **Anti-flapping**: scale-down stabilization window (e.g. 10 min), scale-up cooldown (e.g. 60 s),
  and alarm on scaling activity itself (`scaling_activity > threshold` = you have a loop).
- Use **cluster autoscaler** + **PodDisruptionBudgets** to make sure node scale-up doesn't strand
  replicas of the same pod.

**Common mistakes.**
- Scaling on CPU for a queue consumer (CPU is fine until it saturates; backlog is the truth).
- Autoscaling a stateful workload horizontally with a single writer — you now have a leader election
  problem (12.3.4).
- No drain period → in-flight requests killed on scale-down.
- Scaling down below the number of AZs you need for availability.
- Assuming autoscaling is free. Aggressive flapping and always-warm minimums both cost money.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Traffic doubles every Monday at 9am. How would you scale?"* → Scheduled scaling to be warm
  by 8:55 + reactive backstop + pre-warmed image; note the JIT/cold-start lag that scheduling fixes.
- *"Your autoscaler keeps flapping. Debug it."* → Check the metric for noise (CPU is noisy; RPS is
  stable), increase scale-down stabilization, add hysteresis, and verify you're not fighting a
  periodic external pattern.

---

### 12.1.9 Backpressure and load shedding

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
**Backpressure** = a mechanism by which a slow consumer signals a fast producer to slow down.
**Load shedding** = deliberately dropping or rejecting requests before the system collapses, so
that a *subset* of users gets a fast failure and the rest gets good service.

These are the tools that keep a system *gracefully degrading* instead of *catastrophically
failing*, and they are among the most impressive things to raise unprompted in an interview.

**Why overload fails catastrophically without them.** Queueing theory: as utilization ρ
(λ/μ) approaches 1, response time explodes non-linearly (the M/M/1 result: latency ~ 1/(1-ρ)).
Adding more work to an overloaded system makes it *worse for everyone*, because latency grows and
each slow request holds resources (connections, memory, threads) longer. This is why "just add
more servers" fails during a spike: the extra servers are also slow because the shared bottleneck
(DB, lock, downstream) is saturated. **Overload collapse is worse than overload** — once you pass
the knee, adding capacity can reduce throughput.

**Backpressure patterns.**
1. **Bounded queues** (the simplest and most important). An unbounded queue converts a throughput
   problem into a *latency* problem and then into an *out-of-memory* problem. Every queue in your
   system must have a max size. When full: block (producer waits) or reject (fail fast).
2. **Blocking / handoff semantics** — a fixed thread pool with `CallerRuns()`: the producer thread
   does the work itself, which naturally throttles it. Elegant because it requires no new
   protocol.
3. **Reactive/stream backpressure** — Reactive Streams `request(n)`: consumers declare demand;
   producers never emit more than requested. This is demand-driven flow control and is the cleanest
   formal answer.
4. **Credit-based flow control** (Kafka, gRPC): a receiver grants credits; sender sends at most
   that much. Prevents unbounded buffering.
5. **Adaptive concurrency limits** — TCP congestion control for your service mesh: measure
   end-to-end latency (or queue depth) and adjust in-flight request limits up/down. This is how
   gRPC and Envoy handle overload elegantly.

**Load-shedding patterns, in increasing order of politeness.**
1. **Queue depth / concurrency limit at the edge** — cap in-flight requests; excess → 429/503 fast.
2. **Priority shedding** — shed low-priority work first (analytics, prefetch, background, batch)
   to protect the user-facing path. This is *the* pattern for graceful degradation.
3. **Deadline propagation** — every request carries a deadline (ms); downstream hops check it and
   fail immediately rather than working on a request nobody is waiting for. This is the most
   powerful and least-implemented technique, and naming it in an interview is a strong signal.
4. **Degradation / graceful degradation ladder** — when load is high, serve cached data, drop
   recommendations, reduce result counts, disable comments. Say this out loud: "the design should
  include a documented degradation ladder."
5. **Random sampling** — serve 1 in N expensive requests. Keeps aggregate statistics valid.
6. **Token-bucket rejection with Retry-After** — shed with a precise signal so clients back off
  correctly rather than retry-storming.

**Retry storms and the correct client behavior.** Retries during an outage turn a 2× spike into a
50× spike. Correct clients use:
- **Exponential backoff with full jitter**: `sleep = random(0, min(cap, base * 2^attempt))`.
  Full jitter (not equal jitter) is what actually breaks synchronization.
- **Retry only idempotent/safe operations** (GET; or POST with an idempotency key).
- **Retry budget** — cap retries at a % of total requests (e.g. 10–20%) so the system reserves
  capacity for first-time traffic.
- **Circuit breakers** on the client side to stop hammering a dead dependency.
- **Deadline/budget propagation** so retries share a total time budget instead of each retry
  getting a fresh full timeout.

**Bulkheads.** Isolate resource pools per feature/tenant/deps so a starving component cannot
consume all threads/connections. Thread-pool-per-dependency, separate connection pools, separate
deployments for critical vs non-critical paths. Named after ship compartments — the analogy lands
well in interviews.

**When to use vs not.**
- Always use bounded queues. Non-negotiable.
- Use priority shedding when you have a mix of user-facing and background work.
- Use deadline propagation when request chains span multiple services and you care about tail
  latency (which you always should).
- Don't hand-roll this where a battle-tested library (resilience4j, Polly, Envoy, KEDA, service
  mesh) does it; do *design* it, don't *implement* it.

**Trade-offs.**
- Load shedding improves overall availability but increases error rates for a subset — you must be
  able to explain and defend *who* gets shed (per-tenant fairness; never shed paid/pro tier
  first).
- Backpressure that blocks propagates latency; backpressure that rejects propagates errors. Pick
  per boundary: block for internal consumers (protects the DB), reject at the API edge (protects
  the user experience with a fast, honest error).
- Aggressive deadlines + retries can cause spurious errors; set deadlines from measured p99, not
  from the max.

**Common mistakes.**
- Unbounded queues / unbounded goroutines. The single most common cause of cascading collapse.
- Retrying non-idempotent writes without an idempotency key (double charges).
- Load shedding with 429s but no `Retry-After`, so clients hammer.
- No per-tenant fairness: the loudest tenant gets shed first, which is a business problem.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Your service is at 100% CPU and latency is climbing. What do you do, in order?"* → (1) shed,
  (2) add concurrency limits/backpressure to stop the queue from growing, (3) scale out only if
  the bottleneck is per-instance (if it's the DB, more instances make it worse), (4) kill
  expensive features per the degradation ladder, (5) find the actual bottleneck.
- *"How would you protect the database from a traffic spike?"* → Connection proxy with a hard pool
  cap, a request queue limit, backpressure (block at the app, not grow the DB pool), read-only
  mode for non-essential writes, and shedding at the edge.

---

### 12.1.10 Multi-region: active-active vs active-passive, and data residency

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
Multi-region means serving from more than one geographic region (typically ≥3, or 2 + 1).
Two topologies: **active-active** (all regions serve live traffic) and **active-passive** (one
region serves, others stand by for failover).

**The fundamental constraint: latency vs consistency.**
A cross-region round trip is ~70–150 ms (inter-continent), and writes must often be replicated
across regions. So:
- **Strict consistency across regions** → every write pays cross-region latency (~100–200 ms+ of
  added p99) or you risk data loss on failover.
- **Eventual/async replication** → low local latency, but you must accept stale reads and, on
  failover, **lost writes** (RPO = replication lag, e.g. seconds) or you must promote with conflict
  risk.

You cannot have low latency, global consistency, and high availability simultaneously. Pick two.

**Active-passive.**
- One writer region (plus a hot standby). Reads served from the primary region, or from a read-only
  standby with staleness.
- Simple, consistent (single writer ⇒ no write conflicts), failover is a DNS/Anycast/GLB switch.
- Costs: you pay for idle capacity; failover takes as long as promotion (replica promotion, DNS
  TTL propagation, cache warming — think minutes); you're regionally-vulnerable during an outage.
- Use when: strict consistency, regulatory constraints, or a workload with a clear home region.

**Active-active.**
- Multiple regions serve live traffic. Reads are local (fast). Writes go to the home region of the
  data (routed) or are reconciled.
- Gains: low latency for everyone, no single region failure = no failover event, and you can drain
  a region for maintenance with no user impact.
- Costs: **write conflict resolution is now your problem.** Techniques:
  - **Home-region routing / cell-based architecture**: each piece of data has a home region;
    writes go there. You lose write locality for cross-region users but keep consistency.
  - **Last-write-wins with logical clocks**: simple, loses data on concurrent writes, but is
    acceptable for many products (e.g. settings, "last position").
  - **Multi-master with conflict resolution**: Dynamo-style, vector clocks, CRDTs (12.3.14) — full
    availability, eventual convergence, harder to reason about.
  - **Operational transform / CRDT for text**: for collaborative documents.
- Use when: global audience with latency SLOs, or availability is paramount.

**Failover mechanics (the part interviewers dig into).**
- **DNS failover** — slow (TTL-bounded), client-cached, so minutes. Use short TTLs (30–60 s) and
  accept it, or use a global LB / anycast.
- **Anycast** — a single IP announced from multiple PoPs; BGP does the routing and the failure
  detection (sub-second). This is how CDNs and DNS services get fast failover. Trade-off: you lose
  the ability to pin a user to a region for consistency.
- **Client-driven / client-side failover** — clients are configured with an ordered list of
  endpoints and try the next on failure. Fastest and most robust, but requires you to update
  clients, which you don't always control.
- **Database promotion** — the hard part: promoting a replica makes it a writer; you must fence
  the old primary (STONITH / a lease / a distributed lock) or you have split-brain (12.3.13).

**Data residency and compliance.**
- GDPR, CCPA, and sector rules (HIPAA, PCI, India DPDP, China PIPL) can require personal data to
  be stored and processed in a specific geography, and can restrict cross-border transfer.
- Practical designs: **regional cells** (a full independent stack per region with data staying put),
  **data residency routing** (route the request to the region where that user's data lives), or
  **field-level/tokenization** (store a token globally, PII regionally).
- Engineering implications: your data model needs a home region per record; cross-region analytics
  require a lawful mechanism (federated query, regional aggregation then transfer, or a
  privacy-preserving copy). **Say explicitly which of your data is regionalized and which is
  global** — that is the question a compliance-minded interviewer is really asking.

**Consistency-by-design patterns for global systems** (know these by name):
- **Cell-based architecture** — the industry default (AWS, Google): the world is divided into
  cells; each cell is self-sufficient and owns its data; global services handle only a tiny
  fraction of requests (signup, config). Limits blast radius.
- **Home-region per entity** — simple, consistent, costs cross-region latency for a minority of users.
- **Global read, local write** — the pragmatic default for social feeds.
- **Geopartitioned analytics** — per-region warehouse + a global semantic layer.

**When to use vs not.**
- Single region is often right to much larger scale than people think, *provided* you have a
  tested, fast regional failover (standby in another AZ/region, RTO ~ minutes, RPO ~ seconds).
- Multi-region earns its cost when: your users are geographically distributed and latency is an
  SLO; your business requires regional resilience; or regulation requires residency.

**Trade-offs summary.**

| | Single region | Active-passive | Active-active |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Latency | Bad for far users | Bad for far users (passive) | Good everywhere |
| Write consistency | Simple | Simple (single writer) | Complex (conflicts) |
| RPO | seconds (replica) | seconds | seconds to 0 (w/ consensus) |
| RTO | minutes (region failover) | minutes | seconds (no failover needed) |
| Complexity | Low | Medium | High |
| Cost | Lowest | Medium | Highest |

**Production considerations.**
- Test regional failover for real, on a schedule, with a game-day document. Untested failover has a
  100% failure rate.
- The hidden cost of failover is **caches**: they're empty in the new region, so you get a read
  stampede on the origin DB. Mitigate with cache warming, request coalescing, and shed reads.
- DNS TTLs, certificate validity, and CDN cache keys all must survive a region switch.
- Watch for **clock skew** across regions for anything using TTLs or leases — see 12.3.8.
- Compliance: document data location per field/class, and be able to say how you'd honor a deletion
  request across replicas and backups.

**Common mistakes.**
- Assuming a multi-region failover works because the dashboards look fine — it works until a
  dependency (auth, config, a queue) is single-region.
- No fencing on promotion → split-brain where both regions accept writes.
- Building active-active before establishing cell-based isolation and robust idempotency.
- Treating "region" as a fine-grained unit; in practice a region is not a failure domain the way
  you want (a control-plane error can affect a whole region).

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Design a globally-available system; users are in the US, EU, and Asia."* → Cells per region,
  home-region per entity, global ID service, Anycast or client-driven failover, explicit
  consistency budget per operation.
- *"We must be GDPR compliant. What changes in your design?"* → Regional cells, data stays in the
  EU cell for EU users, no cross-region raw-PII replication, per-region delete propagation, and a
  lawful path for global analytics (aggregate in-region, export aggregates).

---

## 12.2 System Estimation

**Section goal.** Estimation is the part of a system design interview you can actually get *good*
at with practice, and the part most candidates are worst at. Interviewers are not checking your
arithmetic; they are checking whether you **ask clarifying questions, **state assumptions, **round
sensibly**, and **know which numbers actually drive architecture decisions.

**The four numbers that drive almost every architecture decision.**
1. **QPS** (reads vs writes) — determines scale-out strategy and datastore choice.
2. **Storage volume and growth** — determines partitioning/sharding and retention.
3. **Object size** — determines bandwidth, memory, and whether to use object storage.
4. **Latency SLO + availability SLO** — determines how many replicas, regions, and how much
   consistency you can afford.

Everything else is derived. Get these four, and you can derive the rest on a whiteboard.

**Estimation discipline — 7 habits.**
1. **Clarify before computing.** "Are these monthly actives or daily?" "Read-heavy or write-heavy?"
   "Global or single region?" Two clarifying questions are worth more than ten numbers.
2. **State assumptions out loud and visibly.** Write them on the board: "Assume 10M DAU, 20% are
   creators, 5% of users post per month, avg 3 photos @ 2 MB."
3. **Sanity-check with the outside world.** Compare to a known product: "Instagram at 500M DAU does
   ~10^4 photo uploads/sec at peak, so our 100 uploads/sec is small."
4. **Round aggressively.** 6.2M → "about 6 million." Detail in your head, simplicity on the board.
5. **Order of magnitude only.** Nobody expects precision from memory. Write "~".
6. **Derive, don't memorize.** Show the arithmetic so it can be checked.
7. **Connect every number to a decision.** A number with no architectural consequence is wasted.
   "2 TB → fits on one modern NVMe server → no sharding needed." *That sentence* is the answer.

**Common unit conversions (memorize these).**

```
1 KB = 1000 bytes      1 MB = 10^6 bytes = 10^3 KB
1 GB = 10^9 bytes      1 TB = 10^12 bytes     1 PB = 10^15 bytes

Bits vs bytes: 1 byte = 8 bits.  A "100 Mbps link" = 12.5 MB/s.

Time:
1 day  = 86,400 s        1 year ≈ 31.5M s ≈ 3.15 × 10^7 s
1 month ≈ 2.6M s        1 hour = 3600 s
DAU→MAU rule of thumb: MAU ≈ DAU / 0.3 to 0.5 (people log in on ~1/3 to 1/2 of days)

Storage:
1 photo @ 2 MB → 5M photos = 10 TB (decimal) / ~9.1 TiB (binary)
1 video minute @ 50 MB  → 100k minutes = 5 TB
```

**A worked "power-of-ten" intuition table** (use to sanity-check, not to quote):
- A petabyte ≈ 1,000 modern laptops (1 TB each). A "hundreds of PB" company is genuinely large-scale.
- 1 B rows × 100 bytes ≈ 100 GB (plus indexes, typically 2–3× total).
- A 1 TB SSD ≈ $100–800 in the cloud; a spinning disk ≈ $10/TB. Storage *cost* rarely kills you;
  storage *IOPS* and *backup/restore time* do.

---

### 12.2.1 Latency numbers every engineer should know

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
Latency = the time for one operation, measured at a percentile. Latency numbers are the
"given" inputs to estimation: you cannot estimate QPS capacity or timeout budgets without them.

**The canonical table (approximate, modern hardware, warm, no contention).**

| Operation | Time | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache reference | ~0.5 ns | |
| L2 cache reference | ~1 ns | |
| L3 cache reference | ~10 ns | Shared, last local cache |
| Main memory reference / DRAM | **~100 ns** | ~100× slower than L3 — the cliff |
| Page fault (minor, from disk-backed page) | ~1–10 μs | |
| SSD random read (NVMe) | ~50–100 μs (50–100k IOPS) | |
| SSD sequential read (NVMe) | ~1–2 ms for 1 GB (≈2–7 GB/s) | |
| Spinning disk random read (seek) | **~1–10 ms** (100–1000 IOPS) | The classic disaster |
| Same-DC network RTT (1 GbE, RTT) | ~0.1–0.5 ms | |
| Cross-AZ RTT | ~1–2 ms | |
| Cross-continent RTT | **~70–150 ms** | Physics (speed of light in fiber) |
| TCP handshake (1 RTT) + request + response | 2–3 RTTs | ~3 RTT for TLS 1.3 |
| TLS 1.3 handshake | ~1 RTT (0-RTT resumption possible) | |
| Local process call / function call | ~1–10 ns | |
| **Same-DC RPC (serialize + network + handler)** | **~0.1–1 ms** | "A remote call is ~1000× a local call" |
| HTTP request to a nearby server (keepalive) | ~1–10 ms server-side | Includes your code |
| Redis GET (same region) | **~0.5–1 ms** | |
| PostgreSQL indexed point query | **~1–5 ms** | ~10 ms if it touches disk |
| MySQL/InnoDB point query | ~1–10 ms | |
| Kafka produce (acks=1) | ~1–5 ms | |
| Elasticsearch term query | ~1–20 ms | |
| MapReduce / large sort on a cluster | seconds–minutes | |

**The big lessons to state out loud in an interview:**
1. **Memory is ~100× faster than disk.** If your working set doesn't fit in RAM, you are paying
   100× on every access. This is *the* argument for vertical scaling and for good indexes.
2. **A remote call is ~1000× a local call.** This is *the* argument for reducing hops and for
   batching.
3. **Cross-region is ~100,000× a local call.** Never put a cross-region call on the critical path
   of a latency-sensitive request; accept eventual consistency instead.
4. **Tail latency is not average latency.** See next point.

**Percentiles and why p99 dominates.**

- **p50** = median; half the requests are faster. **p99** = 99% of requests are faster; 1% are
  slower. **p999** = 1 in 1,000 is slower.
- At 10k QPS, **1% is 100 requests/sec**. A p99 of 2 seconds means 2% of requests take over 2s —
  a visible, reported problem. **This is why you must always state "at what QPS" when you give a
  latency number.**
- **Little's Law (L = λ × W):** average concurrency = throughput × latency. `2,000 RPS × 50 ms = 100
  concurrent requests`. Use this constantly: it turns latency into a connection count, and it tells
  you how many app threads/instances you need. If each instance has 100 threads and handles 1,000
  RPS at 100 ms → 100 concurrent → ~1 thread busy on average; peaks matter more than averages.

**Why tail latency is hard and what to do about it.**
- Head-of-line blocking, GC pauses (JVM: hundreds of ms), context switches, disk seek storms, and
  network packet loss (which triggers TCP retransmit with **exponential backoff up to ~seconds**)
  all create outliers.
- **Hedged requests** (12.3.11): send a duplicate to a second server after p95 has elapsed; take the
  first success; cancel the other. This cuts p99 dramatically at the cost of ~5% extra load.
- Also: connection pre-warming, avoiding GC (allocate less), avoiding sync disk I/O, and tuning
  thread pools so queues stay short (queues *are* latency).

**Timeout budgets.** Set a timeout for every dependency, always. **The rule of thumb: timeout ≈
p99.9 of that dependency under normal conditions, and always less than the caller's own timeout
(leaving room to return your own error).** A dependency with no timeout is a latent cascade.

**Memory-latency arithmetic (why batch).**
Fetching 1,000 rows one-by-one over the network at 1 ms each = 1 second. As a *single* batched
query returning 1,000 rows, the 1 ms network cost is paid once → ~5–20 ms. This single insight
justifies denormalization, materialized views, and bulkheads in most real designs. **Say this
explicitly — batching is the most under-used optimization in interview answers.**

---

### 12.2.2 QPS: average, peak, peak-to-average ratio

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
QPS (queries/requests per second) is the rate at which your system must process work. Three
numbers matter, and you must state all three: **average QPS**, **peak QPS**, and the **ratio
between them**.

**Average vs peak.**
- **Average** = total requests / total time. Useful for sizing *capacity* and *cost*.
- **Peak** = the max sustained rate (e.g. the p95 or max of 5-minute averages). Peak sizes your
  fleet, your autoscaling max, your database's ability to survive a spike.
- **Instantaneous peak** (per-second) is 1.5–3× the 5-minute peak. Design for a blip of that too.

**Peak-to-average ratio (PAR).**
PAR = peak / average. It's a property of the *product's user behavior*, not of your system.

| System type | Typical PAR | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Internal B2B tool | 1.2–2× | Business hours only, small n |
| Consumer app, global | 2–4× | Time zones smooth the curve |
| US-only consumer app | 4–8× | Everyone awake at 9am local |
| News/launch/event day | 10–50× | News traffic is spiky by nature |
| Gaming (live event) | 20–100×+ | Everyone plays at once |
| Ad serving (auction) | 2–5×, but bid bursts are worse | |

**How to estimate peak in an interview.**
1. **Time-zone smoothing**: if users are global, peak QPS ≈ average × (24/N_timezones_active) —
   roughly, a global product's peak is close to its average.
2. **Diurnal curve**: draw a sine-like curve, put peak at ~15:00 local, and mark peak-to-average
   ≈ 3–4× for a global product, 5–8× for a single-region product.
3. **Event spikes**: for a launch or a viral moment, explicitly ask "what's the worst hour?"
   and multiply by 5–20×.
4. **Diwali/Black Friday style**: if the domain has a known peak (retail, sports, election),
   the spike factor can be 10–100× and it should dominate your design. Ask about it.

**Worked conversion: DAU → QPS.**
```
DAU = 10,000,000
Assume each user makes 20 requests/day (mix of reads/writes).
Total requests/day = 2 × 10^8.
Average QPS = 2 × 10^8 / 86,400 ≈ 2,300 QPS.
US-only traffic (~30% of DAU), peak-to-average ≈ 6.
  → US average ≈ 690 QPS, US peak ≈ 4,100 QPS.
Global peak ≈ 2,300 × 3 ≈ 7,000 QPS.
Design target: 2× global peak ≈ 15,000 QPS for the read path.
```
**The reasoning to narrate:** "I'm computing average first, then applying a peak-to-average ratio
based on global time-zone spread, then a 2× headroom factor for surprise traffic. My read path
must survive 15k QPS; my write path is a different number because writes are 10% of reads."

**Read:write ratio.** Typically 10:1 to 100:1 for read-heavy products, and near 1:1 for chat or
collaborative apps. This single number determines whether read replicas are your primary tool.
