**Storage → QPS for chat.** Messages are created at the same rate they're read (each message is
read ~once by each of N participants). So:
```
100M DAU × 30 messages/day = 3 × 10^9 messages/day
Average writes = 3 × 10^9 / 86,400 ≈ 35k msg/sec
Peak ≈ 3–5× → 100–170k msg/sec writes  ← this is a serious number
Average reads: if each message is read by ~10 people → ~350k reads/sec, peak ~1M+ reads/sec
```
**Takeaway:** chat is write-heavy at the storage layer. You need a log-structured store (Kafka /
DynamoDB), partitioning by conversation, and fan-out on read or fan-out on write (see the write
path choice). This is exactly the kind of "the number changes the architecture" moment
interviewers reward.

**QPS estimation must always be broken down by operation.** Never say "10k QPS." Say "8,500 read
QPS, 900 write QPS, 400 search QPS, 200 media-fetch QPS" — because each will land on different
tiers and different bottlenecks.

**Trade-offs / what peak drives.**
- Peak drives: instance count (autoscale max), database capacity, cache size (must hold the peak
  working set, not the average), rate limits, and cost.
- Average drives: monthly cost, storage growth, batch job windows.

**Common mistakes.**
- Sizing for average QPS. Your system fails during peak — always.
- Sizing for peak with *no* headroom, so autoscaling latency (which is minutes) is fatal.
- Forgetting that a cache miss storm at peak is your real capacity number ("I can handle 15k QPS
  only if the cache hit rate is >95%; at 50% hit rate the DB sees 7.5k QPS").
- Not distinguishing request QPS from *query* QPS (one API call may be 5 DB queries — the N+1
  problem shows up here).
- Forgetting background jobs (compaction, reindexing, email) compete for the same resources.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How will you size for launch day?"* → Baseline from current traffic × expected multiplier,
  add a 3× safety factor, pre-warm caches, pre-scale, and have a shed plan ready. Ask what
  happens if we're 5× wrong.
- *"Our p99 is 200ms at 1k QPS but 4s at 10k QPS. Why?"* → **Queueing.** Utilization crossed the
  knee; latency is now exponential in queue length. Answer: add capacity *before* the knee,
  reduce per-request work, and shed above the knee.

---

### 12.2.3 Storage estimation: rows, bytes, growth

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
Storage estimation answers: how many rows will exist, how many bytes per row (data + indexes +
overhead), and how fast does it grow? These drive: which database, partitioning strategy, backup
window, retention policy, and cost.

**The method.**

```
Step 1: Entities and counts.
  Users:        10M DAU → ~30M registered
  Photos:       created × retention
  Messages:     created × retention

Step 2: Rows per entity over the horizon (usually "3 years").
  Photos = DAU_creators × posts_per_day × 365 × 3

Step 3: Bytes per row (data + indexes + overhead).
  A row's "true cost" in an RDBMS is typically 2–4× the raw column bytes
  because of: primary key index, secondary indexes, per-row and per-page overhead,
  MVCC versions (bloat), and free space (typically 50–70% fill on B+ trees).

Step 4: Replicas multiply everything.
  Primary + 2 read replicas + backups = 4–5× raw.

Step 5: Growth and linear-scan feasibility.
  Is this bigger than one machine? One machine (8 TB NVMe) is a LOT.
```

**Step 3 in detail — bytes per row (use these as defaults).**

| Entity | Raw columns | With indexes + MVCC overhead | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| User row | ~500 B – 1 KB | ~1.5 KB | Many small fields, wide indexes |
| Photo metadata (URL, dims, EXIF) | ~1 KB | ~2.5 KB | The **image bytes** live in object storage, not the DB |
| Message row | ~200–500 B | ~800 B – 1.5 KB | Partition by conversation; hot partitions |
| Comment row | ~300 B | ~1 KB | |
| Event/log row | ~300 B – 1 KB | ~2–3 KB | Huge volume, short retention → often columnar/warehouse |
| Transaction row | ~500 B | ~1.5 KB | Never delete (audit) |

**Worked example — photo app.**
```
Given: 10M DAU, 500M registered users (5-year-old product).
Creators: 10% of DAU post; 1 photo per session, 1.5 sessions/day.
  Photos/day = 1M DAU_creators × 1.5 = 1.5M photos/day
  Photos/year = 1.5M × 365 ≈ 550M photos/year
  3-year horizon: ~1.65B photos.

Bytes:
  Image (stored in object storage, resized into 3-4 variants):
    original 3 MB + large 1 MB + medium 200 KB + thumbnail 20 KB ≈ 4.2 MB per photo
    → 1.65B × 4.2 MB ≈ 6.9 PB  ← this is the number that drives the design
  Metadata in DB: 1.65B rows × ~2.5 KB ≈ 4 TB  ← fits on one (big) server

Conclusion (and the narration): "Image bytes are the problem, metadata is not. So: images go to
object storage behind a CDN (near-zero cost to serve, egress is the real bill), metadata in a
sharded Postgres/MySQL or a wide-column store, partitioned by user_id and month."

Cost reality check at 7 PB with 3 variants and 30% monthly active photos:
  Hot-set (30% touched/month) needs to be in cache/CDN → that's the expensive part.
  Storage ≈ $15–20/TB/mo → $100k–140k/mo. Egress dominates if you serve raw images.
```

**Worked example — chat app.**
```
Given: 10M DAU, 50 messages/day/user average (heavy users much higher).
Messages/day = 10M × 50 = 5 × 10^8 messages/day
1 year retention: 1.8 × 10^11 messages
Bytes/row ≈ 500 B → 90 TB raw; with MVCC + indexes ≈ 200–300 TB.
Per conversation/day = (say 20M active conversations/day) → 25 messages per conversation/day.
Hot conversations: a small % of conversations have thousands of messages/day.
```

**The rules that fall out of these calculations (say these explicitly):**
- **Images, video, audio, and documents do not belong in a relational database.** Object storage +
  CDN. Always. The database holds the *pointer* and metadata.
- **If one entity grows to billions of rows, it needs partitioning** — usually by time for
  retention, by tenant/user for locality.
- **Growth rate, not current size, determines partitioning.** If you know you'll cross a threshold
  in 18 months, partition *before* you cross it (migrations on a billion-row table are scary).
- **Row count matters less than bytes and access pattern**, but a billion-row table with no
  partitioning will take hours to `ALTER` and may hit maintenance limits.
- **Retention is a first-class design decision.** Set it at design time. "Delete after 90 days" is
  a schema-level operation if you partitioned by time (`DROP PARTITION`), and a horrifying
  operation if you didn't (`DELETE FROM ... WHERE created_at < ?` in batches, forever).

**Growth and velocity.**
```
Growth: if user count doubles every 18 months (common for successful consumer products):
  Year 0: 10M DAU      Year 3: 80M DAU      Year 5: ~300M DAU
Photo volume grows with users AND with engagement (usage typically grows faster
than linearly at first), so extrapolate a multiplier, not a straight line.
Design target: build for 3× today's load within 2 years, on a horizontally scalable
architecture, so growth is a scaling event and not a rewrite.
```

**Trade-offs.**
- Big rows + few of them: fine (a wide table with 1M rows is comfortable).
- Many narrow rows: row overhead dominates; consider aggregating, columnar storage, or a KV store.
- Columnar (Parquet/ClickHouse/BigQuery): 5–10× compression and 10–100× faster aggregations, but
  terrible for point updates and OLTP. Use them for analytics on a copy of your data.
- Wide-column (Cassandra/DynamoDB/HBase): great write scale and partition-key reads, no joins, no
  ad-hoc queries.

**Production considerations.**
- Plan for bloat: Postgres MVCC leaves dead tuples; autovacuum keeps up until it doesn't. Monitor
  `pg_stat_user_tables.n_dead_tup` and table bloat. Routine `VACUUM`/compaction is not optional.
- Backups scale with data. 10 TB → hourly backups are 240 TB/mo of snapshot storage unless you
  use incremental/deduplicated backups (ZFS/LVM snapshots, Zstandard + dedup, cloud incremental).
- Restore time is an availability property. A 30 TB table with a 12-hour restore RTO is a design
  flaw. Test restores.
- Compression (Zstandard on object storage, TOAST/compression in Postgres) changes your byte
  estimates — measure, don't assume. JSONB compresses ~3–5×.

**Common mistakes.**
- Estimating only the "live" rows and forgetting indexes, MVCC versions, and replicas.
- Forgetting that object storage *variants* (thumbnails, multiple resolutions) multiply bytes —
  often 2–5× the original.
- Not asking about retention or deletion (GDPR "right to erasure" forces per-row delete capability).
- Assuming a `DELETE` of billions of rows is a background job you can schedule; on a hot table it
  generates enormous WAL and locks.
- Ignoring that backup size and restore time are architecture constraints, not ops concerns.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"When would you need to shard?"* → When rows exceed what fits comfortably in RAM *and* write
  throughput exceeds one machine's ability *and* growth means you'll cross that within the planning
  horizon. Show the arithmetic that gets you there.
- *"How would you implement 'delete my account'?"* → Soft-delete + async purge worker + cascade to
  derived stores (search index, cache, analytics, object storage) + a tombstone so retries are
  idempotent + backstop TTL-based expiry in object storage lifecycle rules.

---

### 12.2.4 Bandwidth estimation

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
Bandwidth = data volume moved per second, typically across the network boundary (client↔server,
region↔region, service↔service). Underestimated bandwidth is one of the most common causes of a
design that works on a whiteboard and falls over in production.

**The core formula.**

```
Bandwidth (bytes/sec) = QPS × average payload size (bytes) × amplification factor
```

**Amplification factor** — the number everyone forgets:
- **Read amplification:** one API call triggering 5 DB queries, or a page that loads 100 assets.
- **Fan-out amplification:** one write that must notify 10,000 followers (chat, social).
- **Replication amplification:** 1 write → 3 replicas → 3× the write bandwidth.
- **Retry amplification:** a 20% retry rate → 1.2× (and during an incident, much worse).
- **Cache miss amplification:** 5% miss rate on a 100× slower operation ≈ 5× effective load.
- **Egress/CDN amplification:** full image on first view, then variants.

**Worked example — image feed.**
```
10k QPS on the feed endpoint.
Each response is a JSON feed page: 50 items × (metadata 500 B + 150 B thumbnail URL) ≈ 32 KB.
Egress = 10,000 × 32 KB = 320 MB/s ≈ 2.6 Gbps.
If the CDN hit rate is 95%, origin egress = 16 MB/s.
Thumbnail images (20 KB each × 50 = 1 MB per page):
  if CDN hit rate 95% → 10k × 1 MB × 0.05 = 500 KB/s from origin (fine),
  but 10k × 1 MB = 10 GB/s to the *user* = 80 Gbps (that is why you need a CDN).
Cost: 320 MB/s × 2.6M s/month ≈ 830 TB/month egress. At ~$0.08/GB ≈ $66k/month.
  → This number usually drives the architecture: bigger CDN hit ratio, smaller payloads.
```
**The narration that earns credit:** "Payload × QPS is my egress, and the CDN hit ratio is the
single biggest cost lever. If I can get the hit ratio from 95% to 99%, I cut origin egress by 60%.
I also compute the payload size from the actual response, including the fan-out (50 items is a
design choice I should justify)."

**Database and replication bandwidth.**
```
1.65B photos/day × ~1 KB of replicated WAL-ish traffic ≈ ... (small)
Chat: 5×10^8 messages/day × ~1 KB = 500 GB/day of writes.
  Replication to 2 replicas → 1.5 TB/day internal traffic. Fine on a private link.
  Cross-region async replication of 500 GB/day = 5.8 MB/s average, but bursts matter:
  a 10-minute burst could be 10× that. Size the link for burst, not average.
```
**Replicas amplify writes.** Every write hits the primary *and* every replica. If you have 5
replicas, your write IOPS cost is 6× per byte. This is why read-heavy systems add many replicas and
write-heavy systems add few.

**Cross-region bandwidth is expensive and finite.** Typical cross-region transfer: $0.02–0.09/GB
and 50–100 ms latency. A design that fans out cross-region (e.g., every social-graph edge
notification crossing a continent) is both slow and costly. Prefer local fan-out with async
propagation.

**Network saturation is a cliff, like CPU.** Above ~70–80% link utilization, packets queue, latency
explodes, and TCP retransmits kick in with exponential backoff. Design for peak × 2–3.

**Little's Law for bandwidth**: in-flight bytes = throughput × latency. 320 MB/s with 50 ms of
in-flight requests ≈ 16 MB buffered per active request path. This is why bufferbloat exists and
why you need queues with bounds (12.1.9).

**Compression as a bandwidth lever.**
- **gzip/brotli** on JSON/text: 5–10× (brotli better, but higher CPU). Always on for HTTP.
- **zstd** for internal RPC/storage: 3–5×, fast.
- **Protobuf/Thrift** vs JSON: 3–10× smaller, plus schema evolution.
- **Image formats**: WebP/AVIF are 30–50% smaller than JPEG at equal quality — often the single
  biggest bandwidth win in a photo product, and it costs a one-time transcoding pass.
- **Trade-off:** compression costs CPU and adds latency (~1–5 ms for gzip on a 32 KB response). At
  100 Gbps of link, you've saved far more than you spent. Always say this trade-off.

**Common mistakes.**
- Estimating only the happy path payload, ignoring fan-out and retries.
- Forgetting that the *client* network is a constraint too — 4G at ~10 Mbps means a 5 MB response
  takes 4 seconds. Design payloads for mobile.
- Not counting CDN origin egress separately from user egress (different price, different need).
- Assuming replicas are free bandwidth-wise.
- Ignoring that a single hot shard can saturate one link while the fleet is at 5% average.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How do you reduce bandwidth?"* → CDN hit ratio, compression, smaller payloads (cursor
  pagination, ETags/304s, delta APIs), image variant sizing, caching, and RPC encoding.
- *"Your cross-region cost tripled. Diagnose."* → Look for a chatty call that crossed the border, a
  cache miss storm, a retry amplification, a fan-out that should be async, or a replica you don't
  need in that region.
- *"A user says the app is slow on hotel wifi."* → Payload size and request count (waterfall of
  50 API calls), not server capacity. Answer with batching, fewer round trips, and compression.

---

### 12.2.5 Availability and the "nines": what each nine costs

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
Availability = the fraction of time a service correctly serves requests, over a defined window.

```
Availability = (total time − downtime) / total time
Downtime % is what you budget for.
```

**The nines table — this is a favorite interview artifact. Memorize the numbers.**

| Availability | Downtime/year | Downtime/month | Downtime/day | Downtime/minute | Typical architecture |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 90% ("one nine") | 36.5 days | 7.2 hours | 2.4 hours | 144 min | Single server, manual restarts |
| 99% ("two nines") | 3.65 days | 7.2 minutes | 1.44 min | 1.44 min | A few servers, some redundancy |
| 99.9% ("three nines") | 8.77 hours | 43.8 min | 14.4 s | 14.4 s | Load balancer + 2+ app nodes + primary/replica DB |
| 99.95% ("3.5 nines") | 4.38 hours | 21.9 min | 7.2 s | 7.2 s | Multi-AZ, automated failover |
| 99.99% ("four nines") | **52.6 minutes** | 4.38 min | **8.6 s** | 8.6 s | Multi-region or cell-based, chaos-tested, SLO-driven |
| 99.999% ("five nines") | **5.26 minutes** | 26 s | 0.86 s | 0.086 s | Cells, multi-region active-active, engineered for graceful degradation, huge cost & complexity |
| 99.9999% ("six nines") | 31.6 s | 2.6 s | 0.086 s | — | Google-scale; requires the product itself to degrade gracefully |

**How to compute your own numbers.** Error budget:
```
Error budget = 1 − availability target.
Monthly (30 days): allowed downtime = 43,200 s × (1 − target).
  99.9%  → 43.2 s/month
  99.95% → 21.6 s/month
  99.99% → 4.32 s/month  (4 min 19 s)
  99.999%→ 0.432 s/month
```
Combine with **availability math**: for independent components, total availability = product of
component availabilities (which is why reliability compounds multiplicatively against you).
```
App tier 99.99% (redundant) × DB tier 99.95% (multi-AZ) = 0.9999 × 0.9995 = 0.9994 → 99.94%.
Your single PG-primary-with-replica caps the whole system near 99.9%.
```
This is the argument for *removing* single points of failure rather than adding redundancy: one
99.5% component in a chain drags you below your target no matter how good the rest is.

**What each nine actually costs (in engineering and money).**
- 3 nines → ~1 senior engineer part-time on reliability; single-region multi-AZ is enough.
- 4 nines → an SRE function, on-call, runbooks, game days, multi-region failover; 2–5× the
  infrastructure cost per unit of capacity (redundancy everywhere).
- 5 nines → cell-based architecture, active-active, formal change management, capacity planning
  for 2× surge events, and a product that *degrades gracefully*. This is a multi-team, multi-year
  investment. Google's paper on achieving 5 nines is the canonical reference.

**Availability is not uptime.** It's *correct* uptime. Returning a wrong answer or an error is a
failure. This is why consistency and availability trade against each other (12.3.1).

**Dependencies multiply.** If your API is 99.99% but it calls a payment provider that is 99.9%
with a 2-minute timeout, your effective availability is limited by the dependency unless you
degrade gracefully (serve cached/default values, queue the call, and report degraded status).

**The crucial interview point: how do you achieve each nine?**
- **99.9:** remove SPOFs (no single DB primary), add a load balancer, automated health checks and
  restarts, monitored deploys.
- **99.99:** multi-AZ with automatic failover, tested failover runbooks, no single points of
  control (DNS, certs, config), blameless postmortems, error budgets gating releases.
- **99.999:** cells + active-active multi-region, formal dependency error handling and graceful
  degradation, quarterly failover drills, capacity for 2× peak on short notice, immutable
  infrastructure, and automatic detection of regional failure.

**SLOs and error budgets (the modern practice).**
- Set an SLO per user-visible journey (e.g. "99.9% of feed requests served in <500ms").
- Track a rolling error budget. **When the budget is exhausted, freeze feature releases** and spend
  engineering time on reliability. This converts an abstract "reliability goal" into a decision
  rule, and mentioning it signals real experience.

**RTO and RPO (the two numbers to pair with availability).**
- **RTO** (Recovery Time Objective): how long before you're back. Set by failover automation.
- **RPO** (Recovery Point Objective): how much data you can lose. Set by replication lag /
  backup frequency.
```
99.99% monthly (4.3 min downtime) + RPO 0 → requires synchronous cross-region replication,
which costs ~100 ms write latency. This is often the real trade the interviewer wants you to name.
99.99% monthly + RPO 60 s → async cross-region replication, cheap and fast locally.
```

**Common mistakes.**
- Quoting a nines target without a number ("we're highly available").
- Confusing HA (high availability) with DR (disaster recovery). HA = uptime; DR = surviving
  region/data loss, needs backups, not just replicas.
- Assuming replicas give RPO=0. Async replicas lose acknowledged writes on failover.
- Not measuring availability from the *client's* perspective (edge errors and CDN 5xx count).
- Multi-AZ in one region is not multi-region: a control-plane or bad deploy takes out all AZs.
- Treating a "status page uptime 99.98%" as your SLO — that's your infrastructure, not your user
  journey.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Design for 99.99%. What's the bottleneck?"* → "Whatever is still a single point — typically the
  database write path or a control-plane dependency. I'd remove SPOFs, then measure and drill."
- *"Your dependency goes down for 90 seconds. What should your service do?"* → Circuit breaker
  open, serve cached/last-known-good, degrade (queued writes), surface a degraded indicator, and
  bound the damage so you stay inside your error budget.

---

### 12.2.6 How to run estimation aloud

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
Estimation *aloud* = a structured verbal routine that makes your numbers checkable, your
assumptions visible, and your reasoning auditable. This is a *communication* skill, and it
matters more than arithmetic accuracy.

**The 5-step script.**

```
Step 1 — CLARIFY (2–3 questions, before any number)
  "Are these DAU or MAU? Registered or active?"
  "Read-heavy or balanced? What's the read:write ratio?"
  "Global users or single region? What's the latency SLO?"
  "Retention? Do we need to keep everything forever?"
  "Is this a launch (small n) or a growing product?"

Step 2 — ASSUME & WRITE (make assumptions visible on the board)
  "Assuming 10M DAU, 30M registered, 1.5 photos per creator per day, 4.2 MB per photo
   across variants, 2-year retention."

Step 3 — COMPUTE (derive, show the arithmetic, round aggressively)
  "1M creators/day × 1.5 = 1.5M photos/day → 550M/year → ~1.1B over 2 years.
   1.1B × 4.2 MB ≈ 4.6 PB of image data."

Step 4 — CROSS-CHECK (compare to reality, sanity-test)
  "Instagram handles ~10^4 uploads/sec at peak; our 1.5M/day = ~17/sec average is far
   smaller, so the image bytes are a storage/bandwidth problem, not a compute problem."

Step 5 — DECIDE (attach each number to a decision — THIS is what you're graded on)
  "4.6 PB of images >> one machine → object storage + CDN, not a database.
   ~1.1B metadata rows → partition by (user_id, month) from day one.
   Peak read ~30k QPS → N stateless app nodes behind an L7 LB, autoscaled."
```

**Announce, don't ask silently.** Say each assumption as you make it, in a short sentence. The
interviewer can then correct you *before* you build three minutes of arithmetic on a wrong
assumption. This is the single highest-ROI communication habit in a design interview.

**Use a table for the assumptions and the results.** Writing a small table
(`Entity | count | bytes/row | total | replicated`) signals structured thinking and makes your
numbers reviewable at a glance.

**Round to 1 significant digit and say "approximately."** "About 5 petabytes" is a correct answer;
"4.73 PB" invites scrutiny you don't need and wastes your time.

**Name what you're *not* estimating and why.** "I'm not estimating write QPS precisely because
writes are 10% of reads and the write path is a different datastore; I'll come back to it when I
design that tier." This shows prioritization, not an omission.

**Handle being wrong gracefully.** If the interviewer says "actually it's 100 billion users," say:
"OK, that scales storage by 100×; the architecture is unchanged for 10 years but sharding becomes
mandatory at month 6 rather than year 3, so I'd design the shard key in from the start." This
*re-derive* behavior is what's being tested.

**Time-box it.** 5–7 minutes of estimation in a 45-minute interview, max. If estimation is taking
longer, you have missed a clarification.

**Common mistakes.**
- Doing math before any clarifying question.
- Silent arithmetic — the interviewer can't see your assumptions and can't correct you.
- Presenting numbers without architectural consequences.
- Over-precision and pseudo-exactness ("4.7 PB") instead of order-of-magnitude rigor.
- Estimating storage but forgetting that replicas and indexes 3–5× it.
- Not mentioning **peak** at all. Always give average *and* peak *and* the headroom factor.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"You estimated 5 QPS. Really?"* → "That's average. Peak is 6× that, and with a 2× safety
  factor I'm designing for ~60 QPS at the edge."
- *"What would change if we had 100× the users?"* → Re-derive: storage 100×, QPS 100×, so
  partitioning becomes mandatory now; the shard key and the CDN hit ratio become the two most
  important decisions.

---

### 12.2.7 Worked estimation: photo-sharing app and chat app

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
Two complete estimations, narrated end-to-end. These are the two canonical "design a consumer app"
prompts, and having them rehearsed means you'll never blank under pressure.

---

#### Worked example A: photo-sharing app (Instagram-like)

**Given:** 10M DAU, 30M registered, 500M total photos to date, globally distributed (40% US,
30% EU, 30% APAC), read:write ≈ 100:1, availability target 99.99%, p99 latency < 300ms for feed.

**Step 1 — Clarify.**
- Feed read pattern: do users scroll an infinite feed (cursor pagination)?
- Max photo size / do we accept video?
- Retention: forever?

**Step 2 — Assume.**
```
Creators: 10% of DAU post → 1M creators/day
Photos per creator session: 1.5 → 1.5M photos/day
Avg photo with 4 variants (orig, large, med, thumb): 4.2 MB
Metadata row: ~1 KB raw → ~2.5 KB with indexes
Feed API returns 50 items × ~650 B of JSON ≈ 32 KB response
```

**Step 3 — Compute.**
```
Photos/day          = 1.5M
Photos/year         = 550M
Total to date       = 500M  → total ≈ 1.05B photos

Image storage       = 1.05B × 4.2 MB ≈ 4.4 PB
Metadata rows       = 1.05B rows
Metadata storage    = 1.05B × 2.5 KB ≈ 2.6 TB  (+ 3 replicas ≈ 8 TB)

Average write QPS   = 1.5M / 86,400 ≈ 17 writes/sec
Peak write QPS      = 17 × 5 ≈ 85 (events spike higher; design for ~500)

Average read QPS    = 100 × 17 ≈ 1,700/sec
Global peak         = 1,700 × 3 ≈ 5,000/sec
With 2× headroom    = 10,000 read QPS at the edge
```

**Step 4 — Cross-check.** Instagram does ~10⁴ uploads/sec at peak with 2B users. Our 85 writes/sec
is three orders of magnitude smaller → this system is **not compute-bound**; it is **storage- and
bandwidth-bound**. That tells you where to spend your effort.

**Step 5 — Decide (this is the answer).**
```
IMAGES     → object storage (S3) + CDN. Multi-variant, content-addressed keys for
             immutability. Never in the DB. Lifecycle to cold storage after 90 days.
METADATA   → Postgres/MySQL, sharded by user_id (co-locates a user's photos),
             partitioned by created_at (retention + time queries). Read replicas for feed
             reads. ~2.6 TB fits on one node; shard early (16 logical shards) so the
             rebalance is a config change.
FEED       → precomputed fan-out-on-read with materialized feed tables per user,
             cached in Redis (top 500 items) + CDN for the shell. Invalidated on new post.
SEARCH     → Elasticsearch index (async via Kafka CDC). Never query ES synchronously
             for correctness-critical paths.
UPLOADS    → client → presigned URL → object storage (bypasses your servers entirely).
             Async: emit event → generate variants → update metadata row.
NOTIFY     → async (Kafka) to push/email; never in the request path.
```

**Numbers to say out loud:** "4.4 PB of images in object storage, 2.6 TB of metadata that fits on
one big Postgres node but I'll shard anyway, ~85 peak write QPS, ~10k peak read QPS at the edge
with headroom."

**Key follow-up probe: "where does it break first?"** Answer: (1) the feed read path if the cache
misses (a 5% miss rate on 10k QPS = 500 QPS of heavy DB queries → cache stampede), then (2) image
variant generation CPU, then (3) metadata DB writes during a viral event. Mitigation for each:
coalescing + warm cache, async workers with autoscaling + queue, and sharded writes + queueing.

---

#### Worked example B: chat app (WhatsApp/Slack-like)

**Given:** 10M DAU, 50 messages/user/day average (heavy tail: top 1% send 500+/day),
read:write ≈ 1:1 in *write volume* but each message is fanned out to recipients, availability
99.99%, delivery latency p99 < 1s, offline delivery required.

**Step 1 — Clarify.** Group chats? Max group size? Message ordering guarantees? Attachments?
E2E encryption? History sync for new devices?

**Step 2 — Assume.**
```
Messages/day          = 10M DAU × 50 = 5 × 10^8/day
Avg message size      = 300 B (metadata + text)
Group chats: 20% of messages, avg group size 8, avg 40 msgs/group/day
```

**Step 3 — Compute.**
```
Average writes = 5×10^8 / 86,400 ≈ 5,800 msg/sec  ← note: NOT small
Peak writes   = 5,800 × 4 ≈ 23,000 msg/sec (design for ~100k during events)

Delivery reads = if each message is delivered to 3 devices on average:
                5,800 × 3 ≈ 17,000 delivery events/sec average
Peak deliveries ≈ 70,000/sec  → long-lived connections needed

Storage: 5×10^8/day × 365 × 1 yr = 1.8×10^11 messages/year
         × ~800 B (row + indexes) ≈ 145 TB/yr  → ~0.5 PB for 3 years

Active conversations/day ≈ 2×10^7 → 25 messages per conversation/day
Hot conversations: top 1% may have >1,000 messages/day → hot partitions, need splitting
```

**Step 4 — Cross-check.** Slack and WhatsApp run in the 10⁴–10⁵ message/sec range at peak. Our
23k/s peak is realistic but not extreme — **this is a genuinely write-heavy, fan-out-heavy system**,
the opposite of the photo app. The architecture must be different.

**Step 5 — Decide (this is the answer).**
```
INGEST     → stateless API nodes → append to Kafka partitioned by conversation_id.
             Kafka is the write path; it's fast, ordered per partition, and replayable.
             This is the single most important decision: it decouples ingest from delivery.
STORAGE    → Cassandra/DynamoDB/HBase, partitioned by conversation_id, one row per
             conversation (wide row) or per message. Ordered within a partition.
             Time-bucketed tables (bucket by day) for easy TTL/retention.
DELIVERY   → WebSocket gateway fleet; fan-out on read from a per-user inbox.
             Fan-out on READ (compute recipients at read time) beats fan-out on WRITE
             (precompute 10k inboxes) unless you must support push to offline devices.
OFFLINE    → Per-user inbox table written asynchronously; pulled on reconnect (sync).
             This is where "fan-out on write" pays off — one write, one inbox row.
READS      → Long-lived connections; need ~100k concurrent connections → need a
             connection-efficient gateway, backpressure, and heartbeats.
ORDERING   → Per-conversation sequence numbers from Kafka; clients dedupe and order.
             No global ordering needed (see 12.3.8).
PRESENCE   → Gossip / ephemeral in-memory with TTL (see 12.3.10). Never strongly consistent.
ATTACHMENTS→ Object storage + CDN, same as photo app.
```

**Numbers to say out loud:** "5,800 writes/sec average, ~23k peak, ~70k delivery events at peak,
about 0.5 PB of messages over 3 years. Because it's write- and fan-out-heavy, Kafka + a
partition-ordered wide-column store beats a relational database, and fan-out happens on read for
online delivery but on write for offline inboxes."

**Key follow-up probe: "why not just use Postgres?"** Answer: "At 23k writes/sec with per-message
fan-out and 3-year retention, Postgres needs heavy sharding, has no native ordered-key streaming,
and its MVCC write amplification makes it a poor fit for an append-only, partition-ordered
workload. Kafka + Cassandra gives ordered per-partition writes, trivial horizontal scale, and TTL
buckets. I could start with Postgres and migrate at ~5k writes/sec — the ingestion abstraction is
the same."

**Key follow-up probe: "how do you handle message ordering and offline delivery?"** Answer:
per-conversation monotonic sequence numbers assigned at ingest; the gateway delivers with a
per-conversation lock (or relies on ordered Kafka consumption and reorders on read); offline users
get an inbox row written asynchronously; on reconnect the client does a `since=cursor` sync, which
is idempotent.

---

## 12.3 Distributed Systems Concepts

**Section goal.** You do not need to implement Raft. You need to (a) explain why a decision is
correct, (b) know what each primitive guarantees and costs, and (c) never say something *folklore*.

**The five things every distributed system must decide** (use this as your mental checklist):
1. **Who decides?** (leader, coordinator, consensus) — or nobody (gossip/CRDT).
2. **How do they agree?** (consensus, quorum, eventual convergence)
3. **What do they do when they can't?** (partition: which side wins? fail-stop vs fail-slow?)
4. **How does time work?** (clocks, ordering, leases, expiry)
5. **How does failure get detected?** (timeouts, heartbeats, failure detectors)

**The two models you must distinguish.**
- **Synchronous / blocking remote calls:** simple to reason about, latency = sum of hops, and one
  slow dependency breaks the whole chain. Use for 1–3 hops on the user-facing path only.
- **Asynchronous / messaging:** durable buffering, absorbs spikes (12.1.9), enables fan-out and
  retries, but you lose immediate consistency and must handle **duplicates, reordering, and
  eventual consistency explicitly**. Use for everything else.

---

### 12.3.1 CAP and PACELC without the folklore

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
**CAP** (Gilbert & Lynch, 2002): in the presence of a **network partition**, a distributed system
must choose between **C**onsistency and **A**vailability.

**The folklore you must NOT repeat.**
| Folklore | Why it's wrong |
| --- | --- |
| "During a partition you choose either CA or CP" | False: *every* distributed system is CA when there is no partition. The choice only exists **during** a partition. |
| "CAP says you can't have consistency and availability" | False: you can have both, as long as no partition occurs. |
| "CAP is a theorem you must obey" | It's an observation about impossibility, not a design rule. And **it's underspecified**: which consistency? which operations? |
| "Redis is AP, Postgres is CP" | Partially true as folklore, and *very* misleading. See below. |
| "My database is CP so it's fine" | Only if you actually fail-stop and only if all replicas are truly synchronous. Most managed DBs are neither. |

**The precise statement.** A system is **consistent** (linearizable, in the CAP sense) if every
node's view reflects the latest successful write, with no stale reads allowed. **Available** if
every request to a non-failing node returns a non-error *without delay*. Under a partition, you
cannot have both.

**The real-world version of the trade: what you give up on the minority side of a partition.**
- **CP (consistency wins):** the minority side rejects requests (503/fails fast). Cost: reduced
  availability for users on the minority side. Correct for: money, inventory, locks, leader
  election, writes to a single source of truth.
- **AP (availability wins):** the minority side accepts writes and reconciles later. Cost:
  **conflicts**, possible data loss, eventual consistency, and *permanent* divergence risk
  requiring a merge strategy. Correct for: "last writer wins" data, shopping carts, likes,
  availability indicators, social feeds.

**PACELC** (Denning, 2010) — the correct refinement, and saying it out loud impresses:
```
If there is a Partition:   choose A or C.
Else (no partition):       choose L(atency) or C(onsistency).
```
Why this matters: **the CAP trade only applies when a partition exists**, which is rare — while the
latency/consistency trade exists *on every single normal request*. So:
> "In steady state, no partition exists and I choose **low latency with bounded staleness**
> (PACELC's E). When a partition *does* happen, I choose **CP for the critical path** (payments)
> and **AP for the social path** (likes), and I have a reconciliation protocol for the AP part."

That is the mature answer.

**Concrete: CP vs AP in real systems.**
- **etcd / ZooKeeper / ZooKeeper-style consensus KV:** CP. Minority side refuses reads/writes to
  avoid stale reads. This is exactly why a ZooKeeper outage can take down the whole cluster
  (everything depends on it, and now there's a partition).
- **Cassandra / DynamoDB (default) / Riak:** AP. All replicas accept writes, hinted handoff +
  read repair + anti-entropy reconcile. You can set R>1 to get read consistency, but writes still
  fan out.
- **HBase / Spanner / etcd on top of a consensus store:** CP-ish with tunable.
- **Most managed databases (RDS):** neither cleanly. Single-writer primary with async replicas is
  neither CP nor AP — it's "CP only if the primary fails fast and you promote a replica, in which
  case you can lose acknowledged writes (RPO>0)". Be honest about this.

**The other CAP-adjacent theorem: PACELC's sibling, and the two you should actually name.**
1. **FLP impossibility:** in an asynchronous system, you cannot guarantee that a *deterministic*
   consensus algorithm will ever terminate if even one process can fail (crash, not Byzantine).
   Real systems cheat with **randomized** leader election and **timeouts** (Raft's randomized
   election timeouts, Paxos's leader leases). This is the real reason "consensus needs timeouts"
   and why **timing assumptions are the escape hatch**.
2. **CRDTs** (12.3.14) — a different escape: give up on strong consistency to get convergence.
3. **Consistency impossibility** — you cannot have both low latency and strong consistency across
   regions (12.1.10). This is a *latency* argument, not a partition argument, and it's the one
   that actually drives everyday engineering decisions.

**State machine replication (the real use of consensus).**
Consensus is not for storage — it's for **deterministic replicas of the same state machine**, so
that a replicated log can be replayed identically everywhere. That's what Raft/Paxos give you:
- **Total order** (all replicas see operations in the same order)
- **Safety** (all replicas agree on the log)
- **Liveness** (the system makes progress when the network is stable)
Terms: **log**, **term/ballot**, **index**, **commit index**, **leader**, **follower**, **candidate**,
**configuration change (joint consensus)**.
Applications: etcd, ZooKeeper, Kafka (KRaft), TiDB, Spanner, CockroachDB, HashiCorp Vault,
NATS, Mesos.

**When to use vs not.**
- Use consensus for: small, critical, strongly-consistent coordination state — service discovery,
  leader election, config, locks, membership, cluster metadata, schema coordination, idempotency
  registries.
- Do NOT use consensus for: bulk data storage (too expensive — every write is replicated and
  committed before acknowledging). Use a partitioned log-structured store there.

**Common mistakes.**
- Saying "CAP theorem means I can't have both" — the classic interview mistake.
- Calling a single-primary-with-replica "CP".
- Forgetting that **AP requires a conflict-resolution strategy**, and if you don't have one, AP is
  actually data loss.
- Assuming partitions only happen in wide-area networks. Partitions happen in **one datacenter**
  too: a bad switch, a misbehaving service mesh sidecar, a firewall rule, a resource exhaustion
  event. Design for local partitions too.
- Not defining the failure model. Byzantine failures (corrupt or lying processes) require much
  stronger and more expensive protocols (PBFT, 3f+1). Most interview systems assume crash-stop +
  Byzantine only for consensus (Raft handles crash-stop).

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Is your design CP or AP?"* → "That question doesn't have an answer for my system as a whole.
  Let me be specific per operation: payments are CP; likes are AP; reads are local with
  bounded staleness. And in steady state I'm choosing latency (PACELC's E), not consistency."
- *"Your app must work during a partition between two racks. What do you do with the
  inventory counter?"* → "Either reserve at the store (best-effort, may oversell) or have the
  minority side reject purchase (no oversell, but unavailable). I'd pick rejection for money and
  reserve for UX, and reconcile."
- *"How do you detect a partition?"* → Failure detectors: missed heartbeats with exponentially
  increasing suspicion time (SWIM's suspicion mechanism), which distinguishes *dead* from *slow* —
  the crucial point, because a slow-but-alive node misclassified as dead creates a split-brain.

---

### 12.3.2 Consistency models

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
Consistency models describe *what a client is allowed to observe* about the order and visibility
of concurrent operations in a replicated system. Knowing this list lets you answer "how stale can a
read be?" with precision.

**The hierarchy (strongest to weakest).**

```
Linearizable consistency (strong, real-time)
   ↓
Sequential consistency
   ↓
Causal consistency
   ↓
Read-your-writes / monotonic reads / monotonic writes
   ↓
Eventual consistency (plus: read repair, bounded staleness)
   ↓
Weak / no guarantees ("last writer wins", "eventual" in the loosest sense)
```

**Definitions and mechanics.**

| Model | What it guarantees | How it's typically achieved | Latency cost | Where it's used |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Linearizable** | Every operation appears to take effect instantaneously at some point between its invocation and response; real-time order respected | Consensus (Raft/Paxos) before acknowledging writes; single primary + read routing | **Highest** (consensus round trip) | Locks, leader election, money, config, uniqueness |
| **Sequential** | All nodes see operations in the *same* order, but not necessarily real-time order | Total-order broadcast; any replica can answer reads | Medium | Some replicated logs |
| **Causal** | Causally related operations seen in order by all; concurrent operations may differ | Version vectors; dependency tracking | Low | Social graphs, collaborative docs (with vector clocks) |
| **Read-your-writes** | You see your own writes | Session affinity, read-your-write hint, or monotonically increasing version in the read | Very low | Nearly all user-facing systems |
| **Monotonic reads** | You never see time go backwards | Pin to a replica for a window; or track a global version and wait | Very low | Feeds, dashboards |
| **Monotonic writes** | Writes by the same client are seen in order | Client-assigned sequence numbers | Very low | |
| **Eventual consistency** | If writes stop, all replicas converge to the same value | Async replication, gossip, read repair | Lowest | Likes, counters, DNS, caches, feeds |
| **Bounded staleness (bounded eventuality)** | Reads return a value no older than a time bound (e.g. "≤5 s") | Version tracking + `wait_for` / retry at the replica | Low | Often the *best* practical choice |
| **Last-write-wins (LWW)** | Conflict resolution: highest timestamp wins | LWW registers with hybrid logical clocks | Lowest | Settings, presence, "last position" |
| **Read-modify-write** | Atomic increment | CAS, transactions, linearizable ops | Varies | Likes, counters, inventory |

**The read-your-writes family — the practical one.** Most "consistency" questions in interviews
are really one of these three:
1. "After I update my profile, will I see it?" → **Read-your-writes.** Implement by (a) routing
   that user's reads to the primary for N seconds, (b) including a client-generated version in the
   request and having the replica `wait_until_version >= v`, or (c) a per-user sticky routing.
2. "Will I see an old version after seeing a new one?" → **Monotonic reads.** Pin to a replica or
   use version fences.
3. "How out of date can this be?" → **Bounded staleness.** State the bound and enforce it with a
   `min_version` parameter that the replica waits for (with a timeout).

**Practical staleness budgets you can cite.**

| Path | Reasonable staleness | Mechanism |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Feed | 1–60 s | Fan-out-on-read, async update, cache |
| Like count | 1–5 s | Async counter, bounded staleness |
| User profile | ≤1 s or read-your-writes | Primary routing / version fence |
| Permissions/entitlements | **0** (strong) — money and access | Linearizable store, small and hot |
| Inventory | ≤1 s with reservation, else strong on purchase | Reservation + strong final commit |
| Search index | 10–60 s | Async index build from CDC |
| Analytics | Hours | Warehouse, refreshed nightly |

**The crucial interview point: consistency requirements should be set per operation, per data
field, not per system.** "The system is eventually consistent" is a non-answer. "Order placement
is strongly consistent; the product catalog is bounded-stale at 60 seconds; the analytics pipeline
is batch" is a real architecture.

**Trade-offs.**
- Strong consistency costs: at least one consensus round trip (typically 1–5 ms in-region) per
  write, plus reduced availability during partitions, plus no read scaling from cheap replicas
  (reads must be consistent too unless you accept staleness).
- Eventual consistency costs: every consumer needs a reconciliation story, bugs become
  *timing-dependent* (much harder to reproduce), and you need **observability for staleness**
  (replication lag as a first-class alert).
- **The surprising cost of eventual consistency:** it moves complexity from the storage layer into
  every read path and every product surface (a button that doesn't immediately reflect the action).
  This is why "eventually consistent" UX requires explicit product design.

**When to use vs not.**
- Strong: locks, uniqueness, money movement, inventory final commit, permissions, config,
  anything where a human would be upset by an incorrect answer.
- Eventual: counters, likes, views, presence, recommendations, feeds, search, analytics.
- Bounded-stale: the default for user-facing reads where "≤5 s old" is acceptable — it gives you
  read scaling *and* a defensible correctness story.

**Common mistakes.**
- Saying "we use eventual consistency" without saying what happens when a conflict occurs.
- Assuming replicas return the *latest* data. They return the latest *they have*, which may be
  seconds old.
- Forgetting that **read consistency is a separate choice from write consistency.** N writes to
  primaries + reads from replicas = whatever lag exists. Name this explicitly.
- Assuming "the database is transactional" ⇒ "the API is consistent". Transactions inside one
  shard say nothing about reads from replicas or about other services.
- Forgetting idempotency in a retried, eventually-consistent system (12.3.5).

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Your user updates their name and then sees the old name. Why?"* → Read from a lagging replica.
  Fix options: primary routing for that user for a short window, a version fence, or a read-your-
  writes session token. Also: don't shard/cache user profile reads without a version check.
- *"Which operations in your design need linearizability, and why?"* → Be specific: idempotency key
  registration, payment capture, seat reservation, role/ACL writes, sequence allocation.

---

### 12.3.3 Consensus: Raft and Paxos at the level you must know

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
Consensus = multiple nodes agreeing on a single value (or on the order of a log of values) despite
crashes, network delays, and duplicates, in a system with no shared memory and no reliable clock.
Raft (Ongaro & Ousterhout, 2014) and Paxos (Lamport, 1998) are the two practical families.

**What you must be able to explain (and no more).**
1. Consensus is only useful for **small, critical, rapidly-changing coordination state**, replicated
   via an **ordered log** (state machine replication). It is not a database.
2. It works by a **leader** ordering operations and replicating them; safety is guaranteed by
   **quorums**; liveness by **leader election with randomized timeouts**.
3. The key safety argument is **election restriction + log matching**.
4. **No two leaders for the same term may both commit** — so at most one log entry per index is
   committed.
5. **FLP**: determinism + asynchrony + one crash = no guaranteed termination. Real algorithms use
   randomness and timeouts to escape.

**The problem statement (say this first).**
"Given N nodes where any may crash and messages may be lost, delayed, or reordered, provide a way
for the group to agree on a totally ordered log of commands, such that as long as a majority is
alive and communicating, the system makes progress."

**The 4–5 node rule.**
Any quorum-based consensus system needs **2f+1 nodes to tolerate f failures**. So:
- 3 nodes → tolerate 1 failure
- 5 nodes → tolerate 2 failures (the sweet spot for production)
- 7 nodes → tolerate 3, but more replication cost and slower commits; rarely worth it.

**Raft mechanics — the pieces.**

```
Server states:   Follower ──(receives AppendEntries from leader)──▶ Candidate
                              │                                      │
                     (election timeout)                     (wins majority) │
                              ▼                                      ▼
                          Follower ◀──(steps down)──────────────── Leader
```

```
Persistent state on every node:
  currentTerm        monotonically increasing; incremented on timeout
  votedFor           candidate ID voted for in currentTerm (once per term)
  log[]              (index, term, command) — the replicated log
Volatile state:
  commitIndex        highest index known committed
  lastApplied        highest index applied to the state machine
Leader-only volatile:
  nextIndex[]        per-follower next log index to send
  matchIndex[]       per-follower highest index known replicated
```

**The two RPCs.**
- **RequestVote(candidateId, lastLogIndex, lastLogTerm)** → grants vote if: the candidate's log is
  at least as up to date, and the voter hasn't voted in this term.
- **AppendEntries(leaderId, prevLogIndex, prevLogTerm, entries…, leaderCommit)** → follower
  consistency-checks by matching `prevLogIndex/prevLogTerm`; appends; updates `commitIndex`.

**The safety rules (memorize these three — this is what "understanding Raft" means).**
1. **Election restriction:** a candidate can only win if its log is at least as up-to-date as a
   majority's. ⇒ **A leader's log contains every committed entry** (this is the heart of safety).
2. **Log matching property:** if two logs contain an entry with the same index and term, then the
   logs are identical in all entries up to *and including* that index.
3. **Leader completeness:** a committed entry is present in the log of every future leader.

**Leader election.**
- A follower that doesn't hear from a leader within a randomized **election timeout** (e.g.
  150–300 ms, randomized) becomes a candidate: increments `currentTerm`, votes for itself,
  requests votes.
- It wins with **⌊N/2⌋+1 votes** and becomes leader; it immediately sends a **no-op heartbeat**
  (Raft-specific, and important — it advances the commit index).
- Split votes are possible → randomized timeouts eventually resolve them (this is where the
  randomness defeats FLP).
- **Pre-vote** (an extension) prevents a partitioned node from disrupting the cluster when it
  rejoins and bumps terms.

**Replication.**
- Leader appends to its own log, then replicates **in parallel** to all followers; it commits when
  a **majority** has stored the entry, then applies it to the state machine.
- Followers apply committed entries in order (they never apply uncommitted entries).
- If a follower falls behind, the leader walks `nextIndex` back until `AppendEntries` succeeds —
  the "trick" that exploits the log matching property.

**Snapshotting & log compaction.**
Raft logs can't grow forever. Periodically (log size threshold, or a `log.compaction` command from
the state machine), a leader+followers take a snapshot of the applied state machine, compact logs
before the snapshot index, and send the snapshot to followers that are too far behind. Problems
worth naming: **snapshot transfer of a 10 GB state machine is a problem**; **a snapshot installed
from a lagging follower could be stale**; and **compaction loses history needed for audit**. Solvers:
incremental snapshots, chunked/sparse snapshots, or a separate snapshot-transfer service.

**Membership changes (joint consensus).**
Changing the cluster membership is itself a consensus decision, and naive approaches are unsafe
(CAP-style partition during reconfiguration → two majorities). Raft solves it with **joint
consensus**: a transitional configuration requiring agreement from both the old and new majorities,
plus a single-server-at-a-time change (`C_new` with one server swapping at a time is simple but
slow; joint consensus is general and safe).

**Read paths.**
Raft guarantees linearizable *writes*. Reads need care:
- **ReadIndex**: leader confirms it still holds leadership by exchanging heartbeats, then reads
  without the log round trip (no writes may have committed since confirmation). This is the fast,
  safe path.
- **Read-your-writes via leadership lease** — simpler but relies on clock bounds (and clocks lie;
  see 12.3.8). Prefer ReadIndex.
- **Lease/read-index caveats**: a deposed leader that hasn't heard from the new majority must not
  serve stale reads. This is exactly why lease-based reads are dangerous.

**Paxos, briefly (multi-Paxos).**
- **Basic Paxos**: proposers send *Prepare(n)*, a majority replies *Promise(not-n, ...)*, then
  *Accept(n, v)*, a majority replies *Accepted*. Guarantees **at-most-one value is chosen per
  ballot number**.
- **Multi-Paxos**: stable leader optimization; the leader learns that a future ballot n is already
  accepted, so it skips Prepare and goes straight to Accept. This is why "Paxos is only practical
  with a stable leader."
- Terms vs ballots vs Raft terms: Raft's *term* ≈ Paxos's *ballot number*; Raft's *leader
  completeness* is the derived safety property that makes the implementation understandable.
- **Reconfiguration (joint consensus)** in production Paxos (Chubby, ZooKeeper) is notoriously
  subtle — one of the hardest parts of any real consensus deployment.

**Comparison: Raft vs Paxos vs production systems.**

| | Raft | Multi-Paxos | etcd | ZooKeeper | Spanner |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Pro | Understandable, strong leader | Optimal messages | Clean API, K8s backing | Battle-tested, watches | TrueTime + consensus → strict serializability globally |
| Con | Leader-centric | Very hard to implement correctly | Single-writer per key; etcd contention | ZAB is single leader; ops burden | Expensive; needs atomic clocks |
| Use for | Consensus modules, K8s, DBs | Research, ex-Paxos systems | Config, locks, discovery | Config, coordination (legacy) | Globally strict DB |

**Why everyone says "don't implement consensus yourself."**
Raft is a paper-simple spec but a **brutal** implementation: you must get log compaction, snapshot
install, membership change, leadership transfer, disk fsync semantics, and read paths right. Every
subtle bug is a data-corruption or split-brain bug. Use etcd/consul/ZK, or a library that has
already paid the price.

**When to use vs not.**
- Use for: small critical coordination state, membership, locks, config, idempotency registries,
  leader election, transactional coordination (e.g., Stripe-style reservation services built on
  etcd).
- Don't use for: user data, event logs, search indexes, blob storage, or anything big. Consensus
  throughput per key is limited by leader commit latency (typically 1–5 ms → max ~200–1000
  commits/sec/key range; real etcd numbers are lower and latency-bound).
- Don't use when you can honestly get away with a bounded-stale eventual design — most systems can.

**Common mistakes.**
- Describing Raft as "nodes elect a leader and the leader does the work" without the election
  restriction and log matching (the safety arguments).
- Saying "the leader must be alive for progress" without noting followers *can* serve stale reads.
- Claiming consensus gives you "availability" — it gives *consistency plus majority availability*.
- Forgetting that **the state machine must be deterministic** (no random, no wall-clock, no
  floating-point non-determinism across replicas).
- Confusing "committed" with "durable" (in Raft, committed = on a majority; with a proper WAL and
  fsync, also durable).
- Ignoring disk fsync: consensus on a machine that acks before the disk writes can lose committed
  entries on power loss. This is a real, famous bug class (the "write-ahead before ack" rule).

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Why 3 or 5 nodes, not 2 or 4?"* → Quorum math: 2f+1 to tolerate f. Two nodes tolerate 0
  failures (you can't form a majority without both).
- *"What happens if the leader is isolated?"* → It stops accepting writes once it can't reach a
  majority (it may think it's leader for a while — that's why ReadIndex exists); a new leader is
  elected by the majority; the old leader's uncommitted entries are discarded. Reads on the old
  leader must be fenced.
- *"Explain log matching in 30 seconds."* → "If a follower has an entry at index i with term t
  matching the leader's, then all preceding entries are identical too — because the leader built
  its log by receiving them from a majority, inductively."

---

### 12.3.4 Leader election

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
Leader election = ensuring exactly one node at a time acts as the coordinator/writer for a
partition, key range, or shard group. It is a special case of consensus (with the log omitted), and
the reason it deserves its own section is that **it's the most common source of split-brain bugs
in practice**.

**Mechanisms, from simple to correct.**

| Mechanism | How | Tolerates | Split-brain risk | Use |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Static assignment** | Config names the leader | 0 failures | None (but no failover) | Deploy-time determinism |
| **File lock / DB row lock** | `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on a leader row, held for the lifetime | DB failover | Yes, if the lock is on a DB that can fail over (locks can be lost!) | Simple, one DB |
| **Lease / TTL row** | `UPDATE leaders SET expires=now()+ttl WHERE id=?` with CAS | TTL + clock skew | Yes, if the clock is wrong | Simple, one DB |
| **Heartbeat + health check** | LB removes unhealthy targets | Instance failure | Yes (split across LBs) | App tier |
| **ZooKeeper/etcd ephemeral node** | Create ephemeral node; watch it; whoever holds it leads | Session loss, with a TTL | **Low** — the ephemeral node is removed on session expiry, and creation is atomic | **The classic correct answer** |
| **Consensus (Raft leader)** | Term-based election + log matching | f of 2f+1 | Very low | Databases, KV stores |
| **Gossip + suspicion (SWIM)** | Everyone gossips; fastest converging node leads | Suspected-dead nodes | Possible but handled via suspicion + suspicion-of-suspicion | Cassandra, Dynamo-style |
| **Consul session lock** | Raft-backed session + TTL | Same as etcd | Low | Service discovery |

**Why the ephemeral-node pattern is the right default.**
- `create("/leader/my-shard", ephemerally=true)` succeeds on exactly one node (atomic create-if-
  not-exists on a versioned path).
- Hold a **session**; the session has a TTL and is renewed by heartbeats. If you die or
  partition, the TTL expires (or the session is closed by the other side after failing to renew),
  the ephemeral node is auto-deleted, and another node's create succeeds.
- On leadership, watch for the node's deletion; when it's gone, stand by.
- **The subtlety that causes bugs:** the TTL must be longer than your worst-case pause (GC stop,
  network blip), or two nodes can both believe they hold the lease. This is the **lease problem**
  and it's fundamentally about (a) clock skew, (b) scheduling pauses, and (c) network delay — you
  cannot make it safe with clocks alone. Mitigation: conservative TTLs, **lease fencing** (the
  resource must reject requests from a stale leader — see below), and **no pausing/suspending the
  leader process** if possible.

**Fencing — the correct way to make leader election safe.**
The pattern: the leader obtains a **monotonically increasing fencing token** (a number that only
increases) from a consensus store. Every write to the protected resource (DB, object store,
external API) includes the token, and the resource **rejects any token lower than the highest it
has seen**. Then a stale leader that was partitioned cannot cause damage — its writes are rejected,
no matter what it believes.

Where fencing tokens are required:
- Any resource that a stale leader could write to after losing leadership.
- Classic example: an old leader thinking it still leads, advancing a Kafka offset or a
  sequence number; a new leader with a higher token corrects it on its next write.
- **AWS EC2 has a "fencing token" concept; Chubby (Google's lock service) used leases and had to
  handle clock problems; ZooKeeper sessions are the common practical answer.**

**Split-brain prevention checklist.**
- Leader must stop serving the instant it loses contact (heartbeat interval + failure detection).
- Every leadership-dependent write must be **fenced** or **validated against a term/epoch**.
- **Never rely on client-side "check if I am still leader"** — it is a race.
- Use **consensus-backed** election (etcd/Consul/Raft) rather than ad-hoc locks for anything that
  writes to shared state.
- **Prefer a lease with a TTL over a permanent lock**, because permanent locks wedge on node death.

**Leader election for the hot-path DB write path.**
Instead of a leader *election*, some systems just accept single-writer by construction (one DB
primary, elected by the DB's own consensus). The application doesn't elect; it routes.
- **Proxy-based (Spanner, CockroachDB, Vitess):** clients get a lease from the consensus store and
  route to the leader for that leaseholder, so writes don't need a per-write consensus round trip.
  **Lease holders must be within 5 seconds of consensus leader** (in Spanner's terminology) so
  the lease is safe.
- **Shard-per-leader:** the leader election unit is a shard, not a server; a node can lead several
  shards, and leadership moves per shard during rebalancing.

**When to use vs not.**
- Use leader election when a component must serialize writes, allocate sequences, or coordinate
  (order matters): sequence generators, schedulers, indexers, single-writer queues, partition
  owners, singleton cron jobs (with a lock).
- Don't elect a leader for a stateless component — it's just a load balancer (12.1.2).
- Prefer **queue-based work distribution** (Kafka consumer groups) over leader election when the
  work is divisible: partitioning already gives you assignment, failure detection, and rebalancing
  for free.

**Trade-offs.**
- Leaders simplify reasoning (total order, single writer, no write-write conflicts) but create a
  hot spot and require failover machinery.
- Leaderless (Dynamo-style) gives better write availability and no failover latency, but you must
  implement conflict resolution, read repair, and hinted handoff yourself.
- Single-writer-per-key also means **per-key throughput is capped** by the leader. If you need
  100k writes/sec on one key, leader election won't help — you need to shard the key (12.1.5 hot
  key) or accept last-write-wins with multiple writers.

**Common mistakes.**
- Using an ad-hoc lock (`SETNX` on Redis without a TTL) → wedged forever on crash, or double
  leader if the TTL expires.
- Ignoring GC pauses / stop-the-world when sizing lease TTLs — the classic source of dual leaders.
- Not fencing writes from a deposed leader (you *will* get corruption from an expired-then-revived
  leader).
- Electing a leader per *request* instead of per *shard* (thundering re-election).
- Forgetting to handle "leader holds the lease but the clock jumps backwards".

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Two nodes both think they're the leader. What went wrong and how do you prevent it?"* → Lease
  expired during a pause/partition and both believe they hold it. Fix: longer TTL + fencing tokens
  + no process suspension + monotonic local clocks.
- *"How do you ensure only one cron runs per hour across 50 instances?"* → etcd/Consul lock with a
  TTL held for the duration, plus fencing on the side effect (e.g. an idempotency key on the
  output), so even a double-run is harmless. **Making the side effect idempotent is the real fix.**

---

### 12.3.5 Idempotency as a primitive

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
An operation is **idempotent** if performing it more than once has the same effect as performing it
once. In distributed systems, **at-least-once delivery is the default** (Kafka, SQS, most
retriable RPC), so nearly every write path must be idempotent or it must be made so.

**Why it's not optional.**
- Timeouts create ambiguity: the client doesn't know if the write landed. So it retries.
- Retries happen at *every* layer: your code, SDKs, service mesh sidecars (Envoy retries!), load
  balancers, DNS resolvers, message brokers (consumer at-least-once).
- Result: **duplicate charges, duplicate orders, duplicate emails, double-posted rows.**

**Mechanics — the idempotency key pattern.**

```
Client generates:  Idempotency-Key: <uuid/v4 or (user_id, business_op, timestamp)>
Server stores:     (key, request_hash, response_body, status_code, created_at)

Flow:
1. BEGIN
2. INSERT INTO idempotency (key, req_hash, ...)          -- unique constraint
3.   ├─ success (new key)     → execute the business logic, save response, COMMIT
4.   └─ unique violation      → ROLLBACK
5.   SELECT response FROM idempotency WHERE key = ?
6.   if found  → return the stored response verbatim (and 409/422 if req_hash differs)
7.   if absent → 409 Conflict "request in flight" (or, for Stripe, 409 with retry guidance)
```

Key properties:
- **Scope the key per user/endpoint** so one user's random UUID can't collide with another's.
- **Store the request hash.** A different body with the same key is a client bug → 422.
- **Store the response**, so a retry returns the same answer (not just the same side effect).
  This is what users actually want: no double-charge *and* the same receipt.
- **TTL**: keep keys longer than your maximum retry window. Stripe: 24 hours. 24 hours of keys
  × your QPS × response size is your storage cost — estimate it.
- **The insert must be in the same transaction as the business write.** If not, you can record a
  key and then fail the work, and every retry returns "already done" for work that never happened.
  This is the most common implementation bug.
- **Alternative when you can't do DB transactions:** a dedupe record with a state machine
  (`IN_PROGRESS → DONE`), where the response is replayed for `DONE` and `IN_PROGRESS` returns a
  409 + retry-after. Used by payment APIs and booking systems.

**Idempotency in every context (know these).**
- **HTTP:** `PUT` and `DELETE` are idempotent by spec; `POST` is not. `Idempotency-Key` header
  (Stripe, PayPal, Adyen, AWS idempotency tokens).
- **Messaging:** produce with a message key/dedup id; brokers support producer idempotence
  (Kafka `enable.idempotence=true`, exactly-once via transactions) and consumer-side dedupe by
  message ID in an idempotent store.
- **Databases:** `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`; upserts; unique constraints as the last
  line of defense; `MERGE`.
- **Datastores:** DynamoDB conditional writes (`attribute_not_exists(pk)`); Cassandra LWT
  (`INSERT IF NOT EXISTS`) — note LWT is a *lightweight transaction* requiring consensus per
  partition, so it's slow (~10–50 ms); use it sparingly.
- **Distributed systems theory:** **idempotent operations don't need exactly-once delivery** — this
  is the whole basis of the "at-least-once + idempotent = effectively-once" pattern that Kafka,
  SQS, and transactional outbox designs rely on.
- **Jobs/retries:** make the job step idempotent, use a dedupe table keyed by
  `(job_id, step_name)`, or make it naturally idempotent (SET vs INCR; upsert with a version check).
- **Migrations:** expand-contract so re-running is safe.

**The Transactional Outbox — the other half of the pattern.**
Problem: you must write to your DB and publish to Kafka atomically; you can't (two systems, no
distributed transaction).
Solution:
```
1. In ONE DB transaction: update the aggregate AND insert a row into `outbox`.
2. A separate relay process polls the outbox (SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED) and publishes to
   Kafka, marking rows published.
3. Consumers are idempotent, so at-least-once delivery is fine.
```
Trade-offs: outbox polling adds latency (poll interval) and an extra table; CDC (Debezium) is a
lower-latency alternative that reads the WAL; and you still need consumer idempotency.

**Idempotency for the *natural* operations you already have.**
- `SET status='shipped'` — idempotent. `balance = balance - 100` — **not** idempotent.
- `count = count + 1` — not idempotent; `count = max(count, seen_value)` — is.
- "Append line to file" — not idempotent; "write to offset N" — is. This is why log-structured
  systems like Kafka prefer offset assignment (idempotent) over append (not).

**When to use vs not.**
- Always for: payments, order placement, booking/reservation, signup/registration (unique email),
  anything with an expensive side effect and a non-idempotent retryable path, message consumers,
  webhook receivers.
- Optional for: pure reads, naturally idempotent writes (absolute SETs), and low-value operations
  where a duplicate is harmless.
- Use a **unique constraint as the backstop** even when you have an idempotency key: the constraint
  is the only thing that survives bugs, races, and manual retries.

**Trade-offs.**
- Storage cost (TTL'd keys) and one extra write per request (~10–20% more DB writes). Worth it.
- Key choice: client-generated UUIDs can leak (a client retrying with a new key defeats the
  system). Server-derive a **deterministic key from the business semantics** where possible
  (e.g. `user_id + ":" + operation + ":" + business_id`), so a client that forgets can't break it.
- Retrying "in flight" requests return 409, which some clients treat as failure. Design the API
  and the client together.

**Common mistakes.**
- Storing the idempotency record *outside* the business transaction → phantom success.
- Not storing the response → the retry does the work again anyway or returns an error.
- Key scoped globally, not per user → cross-user collisions (a real bug in a famous system).
- Forgetting the webhook receiver (payment providers retry webhooks for up to days).
- Assuming the DB unique constraint alone is enough when you also need to return the same response.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"A user double-clicks 'Pay'. What happens and how do you fix it?"* → Two POSTs, two charges
  without idempotency. Fix: client generates a key, server stores `(key, request, response)` in
  the same transaction as the charge, retries return the original response. Also: the payment
  provider's own idempotency key, and a unique constraint on `(user_id, order_id, state)`.
- *"Our Kafka consumer processed the same message twice. Where's the bug?"* → The consumer isn't
  idempotent. Fix: dedupe by message id in a store, or make the operation an absolute SET.

---

### 12.3.6 Distributed transactions: 2PC and why it is avoided

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
A **distributed transaction** spans multiple resource managers (databases, queues, services) that
don't share a transaction log. 2PC (two-phase commit) is the classic (and blocking) protocol.
**Modern systems avoid 2PC almost universally.**

**Mechanics — 2PC.**

```
Coordinator                                Participants
     |                                       |
     |-- can-commit? query ------------------>|  (vote phase)
     |<-- VOTE COMMIT (or VOTE ABORT) --------|
     |                                        |
     |-- prepare/commit decision ----------->|  (decision is
     |                                        |   FINAL and
     |                                        |   written to
     |                                        |   durable log)
     |                                        |
     (if coordinator dies here, participants BLOCK
      until it recovers -- "presumed abort"
      is the safer variant: participants must
      block until they hear back)
```

Properties:
- **Atomic:** all commit or all abort.
- **Consistent:** if any participant votes abort, all abort.
- **Two blocking failure modes:**
  1. **Coordinator crash after prepare, before decision** → participants are *blocked* holding
     locks until the coordinator recovers. This is the "presumed commit/abort" dilemma, and the
     reason 2PC is called **blocking**.
  2. **Network partition between coordinator and a participant** → the participant cannot learn the
     decision, so it blocks.
- **The performance cost:** prepare is a synchronous round trip that must be acknowledged and
  fsynced; a 2PC commit is roughly 2–3× the latency of a single-node commit, and it serializes the
  whole group. Holding locks across the two phases also means the database cannot simply be
  restarted.

**Why 2PC is avoided.**
1. **Blocking** — a coordinator failure holds resources, converting a small incident into a
   database-wide lock storm.
2. **Latency** — extra round trips inside the critical path of every write.
3. **The "coordinator is a SPOF and a performance bottleneck"** — it must know every participant.
4. **No fault tolerance with heterogeneous/remote participants**: a participant that is
   unreachable at prepare time blocks the whole transaction, even for a read-only participant.
5. **Modern consensus (Raft) can do better**: etcd/consul/ZK *do* implement 2PC-ish semantics
   internally, but they only apply it to a **single, replicated, strongly-consistent log** — never
   across heterogeneous external systems. That is the key insight: *use consensus for one thing you
   control, not 2PC across everything you don't.*
6. **Practical alternative: use one database's transaction** — spend the transactional guarantee on a
   single transactional store (Postgres/MySQL/Spanner), and treat everything else as eventually
   consistent via outbox + saga.

**Where 2PC is acceptable.**
- **Single-cluster, small group, homogeneous, low latency** — e.g. inside a database cluster's
  coordinator log, or a small set of shards you own.
- **Strong consistency demands it** — e.g. cross-shard resource reservation where oversell is
  unacceptable and you control all participants.
- **Strongly consistent coordination stores** (etcd, ZooKeeper, Spanner's external consistency via
  TrueTime + consensus). Spanner notably gets *strict serializability globally* without external 2PC
  by using TrueTime + Paxos internally and per-key "safe timestamp" waits.

**Trade-offs and the "ACID vs BASE" reality.**
- ACID (single-node, local) is still right for your *own* database.
- Across services: prefer **local ACID + eventual cross-service consistency + explicit
  compensation** (saga). This is the practical, industry-standard answer.
- **BASE** (Basically Available, Soft state, Eventual consistency) is a caricature, but the
  underlying point is real: for many product flows, availability + convergence beats atomicity.

**The compensation ≠ rollback distinction — the most important idea in this section.**
- A **rollback** undoes committed state (only possible if the side effect is reversible *and* you
  can find it).
- A **compensation** is a *new, forward* action that semantically undoes the first, in a system with
  no atomic rollback. Semantically different and often different in kind.
```
Bad compensation design:  Refund by DELETEing the payment row.
Good compensation design: Refund = a new credit transaction in the ledger, referencing the
                           original. Append-only ledgers are how real money systems stay correct.
```
Rules for compensations:
- They can themselves fail → they need retries, DLQs, and alerts.
- They must be **idempotent** (a duplicate refund is a real-world disaster).
- They must be **visible to users** ("your refund is processing") because they take time.
- They can be **impossible** — "you can't un-send the email", "you can't un-charge the card once
  settled", "the physical good shipped". Design the flow to minimize irreversible steps, and do
  irreversible steps **last** (e.g., in a checkout, capture payment *after* inventory is reserved,
  and ship only after capture succeeds).
- **Saga coordinator must be durable**, otherwise the whole recovery story is lost.

**Comparison of approaches.**

| Approach | Atomic? | Latency | Availability | Complexity | Use when |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2PC | Yes | High (extra RTTs) | Low; blocks on failure | Medium | Small homogeneous group you own |
| XA / JTA (2PC variant) | Yes | High | Low | High | Legacy enterprise integration |
| **Saga (orchestration)** | No, eventually consistent | Low | **High** | Medium–High | Cross-service business flows |
| **Saga (choreography)** | No | Low (async) | **High** | Low code, high emergent complexity | Many simple consumers |
| Transactional outbox | Local only | Low | High | Medium | Reliably publishing DB changes |
| Two-generations / reconciliation | No | Lowest | Highest | High | Payments, ledgers, ML data |
| "Do the write twice, idempotently" | Effectively yes | Low | High | Low | Crude but effective |

**Common mistakes.**
- Trying to make a saga look atomic. It isn't, and pretending it is produces impossible code.
- Ordering the irreversible step (charge, ship, notify) before the reversible ones.
- Compensations that aren't idempotent → duplicate refunds on retry.
- Assuming your saga is atomic across *all* steps — you must decide, per step, what happens if that
  step fails after others succeeded, and write it down.
- Forgetting that the saga's *happy path* also has partial-failure variants (step 3 of 5 fails
  after steps 1–2 succeeded) — that's what a saga is.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How do you keep a distributed inventory system from overselling?"* → Reservation +
  per-item holding with a TTL, strongly consistent reservation store (or 2PC/lock per item),
  final commit on checkout, expiry releases abandoned holds, and a reconciliation job.
- *"Your saga is stuck. How do you find out?"* → The saga store is a durable state machine; alert on
  non-terminal sagas older than a threshold; provide manual intervention/repair tooling; and make
  every step re-entrant.

---

### 12.3.7 Saga: choreography vs orchestration

🔟 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
A **saga** (Garcia-Molina & Salem, 1987) is a distributed transaction decomposed into a sequence of
local transactions, each with a **compensating** action, coordinated either by messages
(**choreography**) or by a central orchestrator (**orchestration**). There is no blocking, no
2PC, and full availability; the cost is **eventual consistency and visible intermediate states**.

**The canonical example — order placement.** (Note the ordering of irreversible steps!)
```
  1. Create order                (status=PENDING)               [compensate: cancel order]
  2. Reserve inventory           (hold with TTL)                [compensate: release hold]
  3. Charge payment              (capture)                      [compensate: refund ledger entry]
  4. Create shipment             (status=AWAITING_FULFILLMENT)   [compensate: cancel shipment]
  5. Notify customer / ship                                ← NO COMPENSATION POSSIBLE
```
Ordering rule: **do the irreversible step last.** If you charge before reserving inventory, a
failed reservation forces a refund; if you reserve before charging, a failed charge just releases a
hold. This single design decision is worth points in every interview.

**Choreography (event-driven).**

```
Order Service  --OrderPlaced-->  Inventory Service  --InventoryReserved-->  Payment Service
                                     |                                      |
                              <----- InventoryReleased <-- PaymentFailed ---+
```
- Each service subscribes to events and reacts. The *flow* is implicit in the code.
