- **Pros:** excellent decoupling; each service stays independently deployable; no central
  bottleneck; natural fit for pub/sub infrastructure you already have (Kafka); scales.
- **Cons:**
  - **The flow is invisible.** There is no single place that says "here is the process," so nobody
    can answer "what happens if step 4 fails?" without reading five codebases.
  - **Cognitive load grows as participants grow** — "eventual spaghetti." Adding a 6th consumer can
    introduce cycles you didn't see.
  - **Hard to add cross-cutting concerns**: timeouts, whole-flow SLA tracking, and "compensate
    everything" behavior.
  - **Testability**: you must run the whole event topology to test a flow.
  - **Failure semantics are local**: each service must handle partial failure itself.
- **Good fit:** many small, independent reactions (notifications, analytics, indexing, cache
  invalidation); loose coupling; when the flow is genuinely a set of independent reactions rather
  than a procedure.

**Orchestration (saga pattern proper).**

```
                    ┌────────────────────────────┐
   client ────────▶│ Order Saga Orchestrator    │
                    │ (durable state machine)    │
                    └──────┬─────────┬───────────┘
              cmd           │         │          cmd
        ┌──────────────────┘         └──────────────────┐
   Inventory Service                            Payment Service
        (returns result)                        (returns result)
                    │                                │
                    └─── the orchestrator persists the state,
                        advances or runs compensation in reverse
```
- **Pros:**
  - **The flow is explicit, documented, and versioned.** One place to see the whole process.
  - **Centralized timeouts, retries, and SLA tracking.**
  - **Easy to add "wait for X with a deadline", human-in-the-loop approval, and long-running
    processes (days/weeks).**
  - **Easy to test:** the orchestrator is a pure state machine; you stub the participants.
  - Handles the awkward parts: partial failures, compensating in reverse order, and "which step
    failed?".
- **Cons:**
  - Central component = a logical bottleneck (mitigate: it only orchestrates, doesn't do business
    work; shard the orchestrator by saga id so it scales horizontally).
  - Coupling to a specific flow; a flow spanning many teams gets contentious.
  - The orchestrator must be **durable** (a DB-backed state machine, or a workflow engine) or the
    recovery story evaporates.

**Decision rule (say this in an interview).**
> "Orchestration when the flow is a **procedure with a business meaning** — money, provisioning,
> fulfillment. Choreography when it's a **set of independent reactions** — notify, index, cache,
> analytics."

**Saga state machine: the mechanics you must be able to sketch.**

```
SagaState:  PENDING ──▶ RUNNING ──▶ COMPLETED
                          │
                          ├──▶ COMPENSATING ──▶ COMPENSATED (or FAILED_COMPENSATION)
                          │
                          └──▶ FAILED
Per-step state: PENDING / DONE / COMPENSATED / FAILED / SKIPPED / MANUAL_INTERVENTION

Persisted fields:  saga_id, current_step, step_states, timestamps, idempotency per step,
                   deadline, retry_count, last_error, compensation_progress
Timeouts:           each step has a deadline; on expiry → retry (bounded) → then compensate
Ordering:           compensate in **reverse** order of completion (this matters: you can't
                   release inventory before you know payment failed to release the hold)
Persistent guarantees:
  - steps are idempotent (retries happen)
  - the orchestrator's state transitions are transactional
  - "at most once" is impossible here, so design for "at least once + idempotent steps"
```

**Failure modes to name aloud.**
- **Compensation failure**: the original failed *and* the compensation failed → needs a manual
  queue, alerting, and a repair tool. Any real saga system has an admin UI for this.
- **Timeouts**: what if a step neither succeeds nor fails? → bounded retries, then compensate.
- **Long-running sagas**: user abandons checkout → cancel the saga and release holds (this is a
  saga too, triggered by a TTL).
- **Out-of-order / duplicate events** → per-step idempotency keys and a monotonic step counter.
- **Partial compensation**: some compensations done, some not → track per-step compensation state.

**Testing (an interview-adjacent skill worth mentioning).**
- **Test the saga, not just the services.** Unit-test the state machine exhaustively (every step
  fails × every compensation fails).
- **Fault injection:** make step N fail, and assert the end state equals the "compensated" state.
- **Reconciliation/anti-entropy job** that repairs any saga that isn't in a terminal state — this
  is what actually saves you at 3am.
- Timeouts must be exercised with a fake clock; never wait in tests.

**Trade-offs.**

| | Choreography | Orchestration |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Coupling | Low | Medium (flow knowledge) |
| Flow visibility | Poor | **Explicit** |
| Scaling | Natural | Needs sharding |
| Testing flow | Hard | **Easy** |
| Cross-cutting (timeouts, SLA) | Hard | **Easy** |
| Long-running / human steps | Awkward | **Natural** |
| Team count | Many teams, loose | Few teams, one procedure |
| Failure blast radius | Localized | Central (mitigate with durable state) |

**Common mistakes.**
- Starting with choreography for a complex business process, then discovering nobody can explain
  the failure modes.
- Putting business logic in the orchestrator (it should orchestrate, not decide).
- Not making steps idempotent, then getting duplicate reservations.
- Compensating in forward instead of reverse order.
- No manual-intervention path for failed compensations.
- No deadline/timeout per step → sagas live forever holding money and inventory.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Which do you use for checkout?"* → Orchestration (procedure, money, long-running, needs
  timeouts and manual repair).
- *"How would you notify users of a failed order?"* → Choreography (the saga emits
  `OrderFailed`/`OrderCompensated`, and several independent subscribers react).
- *"What if compensation itself fails?"* → Durable saga state flags manual intervention, alerting,
  a repair tool with idempotent re-execution, and a reconciliation job; plus a business rule for
  the worst case (write off the amount).

---

### 12.3.8 Clocks, ordering, and the impossibility of global time

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
In a distributed system, **there is no global clock and no global total order of events**. Every
node has its own clock, they drift, they jump (NTP steps), and a message's send/receive order is
not knowable without extra machinery. Choosing how to reason about time and order is one of the
first design decisions you make.

**Physical reality.**
- Time propagates at ~2/3 the speed of light in fiber. Around the world: ~133 ms round trip
  minimum, one-way ~67 ms. Two data centers 1,000 km apart: ~10 ms one-way. This is *physics*;
  no engineering removes it.
- Crystal oscillators **drift** ~10–500 ppm. At 500 ppm, a machine gains/loses ~43 s/day. So
  `now()` from two machines can legitimately differ by seconds.
- NTP corrections **step** the clock (or slew slowly). A backwards step breaks any TTL/lease you
  computed with the old reading.

**What you cannot do.**
- You cannot order two events across machines by their timestamps alone (clock skew > event gap).
- You cannot rely on a TTL computed from `now() + 30s` to be *exactly* 30 s anywhere.
- You cannot use wall-clock time to decide "who is newer" without accepting a skew window.

**What to use instead — the ordering toolbox.**

| Tool | What it gives you | Mechanism | Cost / catch |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Physical time (wall clock)** | Human-meaningful timestamps, expiry, billing | NTP-synced `now()` | Skew; NTP steps; **never use for correctness** |
| **Monotonic clock** | Accurate durations within one process | `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` / `time.Since` | Only local; useless across machines |
| **Lamport happens-before** | A partial order consistent with causality | Counter + rule: if A→B then A<B | **Not** wall-clock comparable; equal counters → concurrent |
| **Vector clock** | Full causal history per event | Per-node counter map | Metadata grows O(nodes); O(log n) with "version vectors" |
| **Hybrid Logical Clock (HLC)** | Timestamps that respect causality *and* track physical time | Physical + logical counters, updated on send/receive | **The practical modern answer**; used by CockroachDB, MongoDB, YDB, Spanner-adjacent systems |
| **Total order via consensus** | A real global total order on committed writes | Raft log index | Only for the consensus group; costs a round trip |
| **Logical sequence number** | Per-entity ordering | DB sequence / Redis INCR / partition offset | Per-key only; not global |
| **Version vector / dotted version vectors** | Causal tracking for CRDTs | Per-replica version map | Best convergence guarantee |

**Lamport clocks — the mechanics.**
```
Rule 1: on a local event, L = L + 1
Rule 2: on send, send L with the message
Rule 3: on receive, L = max(L, message_L) + 1
Property: if A → B (causally), then L(A) < L(B).
Non-property: L(A) < L(B) does NOT imply A → B (they may be concurrent).
```
So Lamport gives you a **consistent total order** (tie-break with node ID) that respects
causality — good enough to order events in a log, but **not enough to detect causality
problems**. For that you need vector clocks.

**Vector clocks — the mechanics.**
```
Each node i keeps counter[i]. On event at node i: counter[i]++.
On send: attach your full vector.
On receive from j: counter[k] = max(counter[k], incoming[k]) for all k; then counter[j]++.

Compare two vectors A and B:
  A dominates B  →  A happens-after B (A's event is causally later)
  A concurrent with B →  neither dominates (the DANGER: this is what you must resolve)
```
Vector clocks detect concurrency precisely, but cost O(nodes) metadata. **Dynamo-style systems use
vector clocks to detect concurrent sibling versions**, then resolve (usually "return both siblings"
to the client, or LWW if you don't care).

**HLC — what to actually build.**
An HLC produces a timestamp `(physical, logical)` that:
1. is monotonically increasing per node,
2. respects happens-before across nodes,
3. stays close to physical time (so TTLs and human-readable timestamps still work).

Mechanics sketch: keep `(l, c)`; on local event, if `now > l` then `l = now; c = 0` else
`c++`. On receive from `l_m`, `l = max(l, l_m)`; if `l_m > l` then `c = 0` else `c++`.
Use it for: version stamps in a sharded DB, last-write-wins registers, and consistency checks
where you want "ordered but not wall-clock-exact."

**Leases and the lease problem (the important practical one).**
A lease says "node X may act as leader for 10 seconds." It's only safe if:
1. **Clocks are bounded** (|skew| ≪ TTL/2) — via NTP with a max-error bound or a monotonic clock +
   an external time authority (Google's TrueTime gives ±0.5 ms via GPS+atomic clocks and active
   queries).
2. **Processes don't pause** shorter than... actually *longer* than the TTL margin: a GC pause or a
   process freeze must not exceed TTL − max skew, or a stale leader resumes believing it's still
   the leader.
3. **The resource validates the lease** — hence **fencing tokens** (12.3.4). If the protected
   resource rejects stale tokens, then lease safety no longer depends on clocks at all. **This is
   the practical answer, and it's the right thing to say in an interview.**

**TrueTime and Spanner** (the elegant version).
Google's Spanner uses **TrueTime**, which returns an *interval* `[earliest, latest]` (guaranteed to
contain the true time) rather than an instant, with error ~±0.5 ms (or ~±10 ms on non-GPS hardware).
With intervals, you can build **strict serializability globally** without external 2PC: commit waits
until `TT.now().latest` before making a write visible, so all participants know any transaction
with a smaller timestamp committed first. The cost: waiting ≈ the uncertainty window (sub-ms to
10 ms), and dependence on custom hardware.

**Time in practice — what you should actually do in production.**
- Use wall clock only for display, billing periods, and human-meaningful TTLs (with slack).
- Use monotonic clocks for all timeouts, rate limit windows, and duration measurement *within* a
  process.
- Use database/Redis server time or a sequence for cross-node ordering of business events.
- Use HLC or a DB version for cross-node conflict resolution.
- Assume clock skew of ±1 s at minimum in any distributed design and size TTLs accordingly.
- **Set TTLs with generous slack** (e.g., "30 s TTL" implemented as `60 s`) because early expiry is a
  correctness bug; late expiry is just staleness.

**Ordering for the user-facing log (feeds, chat, notifications).**
- You don't need a global total order; you need **per-entity order** (per conversation, per feed).
- Assign a **per-entity sequence number** at ingest (DB sequence, Redis INCR, or the Kafka
  partition offset). Deliver with that sequence, let clients dedupe and reorder, and expose a
  `since=cursor` API.
- This is far cheaper than a global order and is what real systems do.

**Common mistakes.**
- Using `System.currentTimeMillis()` across machines to decide who is newer → silent data loss
  from clock skew.
- Assuming NTP keeps clocks within milliseconds — verify; NTP can fail, and VMs often have worse
  than you think (though modern cloud clocks are good).
- Timeouts computed from a single short TTL for a chain of 5 calls → the fifth call always times
  out first. **Use a deadline propagated down the chain** (12.3.11).
- Naive `lastModified` comparison for cache invalidation with multi-server clock skew → flapping
  cache misses or stale forever.
- Ignoring that a backwards clock step breaks in-flight leases → dual leaders.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Two servers write the same field. Who wins?"* → "I'll stamp writes with an HLC (or use the DB's
  monotonic version), and apply last-write-wins with a defined tie-break; and I will not use
  wall-clock alone because of skew."
- *"How do you order events in a distributed log without a coordinator?"* → Lamport clock +
  node-ID tie-break for a total order; vector clock if I need to detect concurrency; per-entity
  sequence if the order only needs to be per-entity.

---

### 12.3.9 Quorums: R + W > N

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
With **N** replicas storing a value (usually N=3), a write goes to **W** replicas and a read
returns from **R** replicas. If **R + W > N**, the read and write quorums are guaranteed to overlap
in at least one replica, which is what makes the read observe the write (or a *newer* write).

**The math.**
```
R + W > N    →  the intersection is non-empty → read sees the latest write

Examples with N = 3:
  W = 2, R = 2  → 4 > 3 ✓  (Dynamo/Cassandra "quorum" default)  → strong-ish
  W = 3, R = 1  → 4 > 3 ✓  → fast reads, slow writes
  W = 1, R = 3  → 4 > 3 ✓  → fast writes, slow reads
  W = 1, R = 1  → 2 > 3 ✗  → may return stale data (eventual)
  W = 2, R = 1  → 3 > 3 ✗  → may return stale
```

**Monotonic reads requires more than just R+W>N.** The strict condition for
**monotonic-read consistency** (no stale reads after a fresh one) is `W + R > N` **and**
`R > W/2` (equivalently `W + R > N` and `W + R ≥ N+1` with the extra bound). The practical takeaway:
**asymmetric quorums (e.g. W=1, R=N) can return stale reads even when R+W>N**, because successive
reads may hit different subsets. So "R+W>N is sufficient" is folklore that is good enough for an
interview but worth qualifying: real systems use **sloppy quorum** (which returns a value from any
replica, possibly stale, to satisfy the read) — and sloppy quorum is why R+W>N alone does **not**
guarantee anything in Cassandra/HBase/DynamoDB with `QUORUM` reads under partition.

**Dynamo's actual answer (and its surprise).**
Dynamo uses **R + W > N** for "strongly consistent reads," but *also* uses:
- **Vector clocks + sibling reconciliation** — concurrent writes produce *siblings*; the client or a
  background reconciler resolves them. This is the "eventual consistency with conflict resolution"
  model.
- **Sloppy quorum** — writes go to the first W replicas in the preference list that are alive;
  if fewer than W are in the preference list, it uses nodes *outside* it and records **hints** to
  forward later. This trades durability/precision for availability. **This is why DynamoDB's
  "eventually consistent" is the default** and why "R+W>N" is a paper-level guarantee.

**Read repair.**
On every read, the coordinator compares the versions returned by the R replicas and **writes the
newest back** to stale ones. This is the mechanism that actually makes eventual consistency converge
in practice, and it means the system gets more consistent the more it is read.

**Anti-entropy.**
A background job (Merkle trees in Cassandra/Dynamo; full reconciliation otherwise) periodically
compares replicas and repairs divergence. Read repair handles hot data; anti-entropy handles cold
data. **Both are needed** — this is a great, specific answer.

**Quorum and latency — the crucial practical point.**
```
Quorum writes cost max(replica latencies) — the SLOWEST of W, not the average.
Quorum reads cost the R-th fastest response, not the fastest.
So W=3 with a slow replica means your writes are as slow as your worst replica.
Mitigations:
  - Hinted handoff for unavailable replicas (accept the write anyway)
  - Sloppy quorum (write to any W alive)
  - Local write quorum + async replication to the rest ("W=1 local, N-1 async") — this is what
    most real systems actually do (leader-based replication), which makes it NOT a quorum system
    at all but a CP-ish system with a single writer.
```

**Consistency levels named by vendors** (know the mapping so you can translate for an interviewer):
- **DynamoDB:** eventually consistent (default), or `ConsistentRead = true` (= R=N, strong per
  read), plus DynamoDB **global tables** with `ConsistencyControls` (eventual or strong across
  regions — strong costs 2× and applies only to the *same* region).
- **Cassandra:** `ONE`, `QUORUM` (R = N/2 + 1), `ALL`, `LOCAL_QUORUM` (only replicas in the local
  DC — *faster but weaker*, and the usual source of confusion).
- **HBase:** `Get` vs `Scan` with `setConsistency`; strong reads (`ROW`/`TIMELINE`) require
  `HBase.regionserver.handler.count` and rely on the region-server write-ahead log.
- **MongoDB:** read/write concerns (`w: 1|majority`, `readConcern: majority|local|linearizable`),
  causal consistency sessions, and read preference (`primary`, `primaryPreferred`,
  `secondaryPreferred`, `secondary`, `nearest`).
- **PostgreSQL:** synchronous replication level (`local`, `remote_write`, `on`, `remote_apply`)
  controls durability of commit across standbys; `synchronous_standby_names` decides whether a
  commit must wait for a standby.

**When to use vs not.**
- Use quorum reads/writes for: counters (must not lose increments), leaderless replication where
  you want high write availability without a leader, and Dynamo-style KV where you've accepted
  eventual consistency.
- Use **single-leader replication** (primary + replicas) when you need ordering, no write
  conflicts, and can accept an unavailable write during failover. This is what relational databases
  and most KV stores actually do.
- Use **leaderless** when you need to write in any DC with zero downtime and can handle conflicts.

**Trade-offs.**
- Quorum systems: high write availability without a leader, no failover event, but you must handle
  conflict resolution and sibling reads; throughput is capped by fan-out.
- Leader systems: simpler, ordered, faster reads possible, but the leader is a SPOF-ish hot spot
  and writes can't proceed during failover (usually 10–60 s).

**Common mistakes.**
- Saying "R+W>N gives strong consistency" without mentioning sloppy quorum, vector clocks, and
  read repair. Real distributed stores give **eventual consistency with automatic conflict
  resolution**, not linearizability.
- Forgetting that W=3 makes writes as slow as the slowest replica.
- Using quorum counters incorrectly: incrementing a counter on read is a bug; incrementing per
  write at W ≥ (N/2)+1 is the pattern.
- Not pairing quorum reads with a staleness *bound* the client can reason about.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"N=3, W=2, R=2. What can go wrong?"* → Unreachable partition/one node down: write needs 2, read
  needs 2 — with one node down you can still do both, which is the design intent. If two nodes are
  down, both fail: you traded availability for consistency. That's a design choice, not a bug.
- *"Design a distributed counter with 100k writes/sec."* → N=3, W=2, increment on write only,
  read = R=2 and take the max; or use a leader-based counter with batching; never count on read.

---

### 12.3.10 Gossip protocols

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
A gossip protocol spreads state by having each node periodically exchanges small deltas with a few
random peers, until every node knows everything (eventual consistency, typically seconds). It is
the mechanism behind Cassandra's membership, Dynamo-style failure detection, HashiCorp's Serf
(Consul), and many monitoring/caches.

**Mechanics.**
- **Membership dissemination:** each node knows all nodes and periodically contacts a random few
  (e.g. 3) to reconcile ("anti-entropy" push+pull).
- **Failure detection:** you can't *prove* a node is dead. You use a **suspicion mechanism**:
  heartbeat round-trip time is tracked as an EWMA; if it exceeds the suspicion threshold, mark the
  node **suspected** (not dead), and inform others. If confirmed by others, mark **dead** and
  re-distribute. **"Suspected" vs "dead" is the critical distinction** — it prevents cascading
  false positives in a slow network, which is what causes split-brain.
- **State dissemination:** "gossip about gossip" — version vectors let a node learn quickly which
  others it is behind, so information spreads in O(log n) rounds instead of O(n).

**Why "eventual consistency" only.**
Gossip converges, but during convergence nodes disagree. For control-plane data
(membership, config, locks), convergence is too slow and too weak — hence **consensus-based stores**
(etcd/ZK/consul's Raft) for coordination, and gossip for **membership and health**. Also:
- **Convergence time is not bounded**; a "converged" cluster can un-converge under partition healing.
- Gossip has **no notion of a majority** → it cannot make safety-critical decisions.

**The practical systems.**

| System | Protocol | Used for | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Cassandra** | Gossip (gossiper) + failure detector | Membership, tokens | Superclass/Concat + PHI accrual detector |
| **Riak** | Riak-gossip | Membership | |
| **Consul / Serf** | Gossip + **Raft** (LAN/WAN) | Service discovery; **strong consistency for KV** | Great example: gossip for membership, consensus for data |
| **Apache Mesos** | Paxos | Master failover | |
| **AWS Dynamo** (paper) | Membership via internal quorum service, not gossip | | "Gossip is good for membership, not for the leader" |
| **Eureka** | REST/heartbeat (client polling) | Service discovery (Netflix) | Simpler, no gossip |
| **Kubernetes** | etcd / Raft (control plane), kube-proxy / iptables (data plane) | | |

**Anti-entropy (the other half).**
Gossip spreads *new* state; **anti-entropy** periodically reconciles *all* state by comparing
digests (Merkle/anti-entropy trees, as in Cassandra's repair). Together: gossip for liveness and
fast propagation, anti-entropy for correctness.

**Cassandra's PHI failure detector — worth knowing by name.**
It converts a distribution of heartbeat inter-arrival times into a **suspicion probability**
(φ). It's much better than a fixed timeout because it **adapts to the cluster's current network
conditions** (on a congested network, thresholds loosen). Cassandra marks a node dead when
φ > configurable threshold (default 8).

**Trade-offs.**
- **Pros:** no central point; very low overhead; scales to thousands of nodes; tolerant of
  arbitrary failures; simple; no coordinator.
- **Cons:** eventual only; convergence time probabilistic; cannot form quorums; security and
  privacy need care (Gossip doesn't fit hostile networks without crypto); harder to reason about
  during partitions.

**When to use vs not.**
- **Use:** membership and node liveness; "who's in the cluster" caches; fan-out status propagation
  (who's processing what); decentralized rate limiters; health-gossip caches in large fleets;
  distributed "have I seen this event?" filters.
- **Use Raft/consensus instead for:** locks, leader election, config that must be strongly
  consistent, sequence allocation, membership *changes*.
- **Don't gossip for:** anything a human will read as authoritative; anything requiring a majority.

**Common mistakes.**
- Using gossip for the data path and being surprised by stale reads.
- Trusting gossip for safety decisions (electing leaders by gossip's view of liveness → split-brain).
- Not implementing suspicion → cascading false positives under transient network slowness.
- Gossiping over a WAN: latency makes convergence pathological; use separate LAN/WAN gossip
  layers (Consul's design).

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How does Cassandra know a node is down?"* → PHI-accrual failure detector over heartbeat
  inter-arrivals → suspicion → gossip confirmation → tokens rebalanced; probabilistic, not proven.
- *"Do you gossip or use Raft for service discovery?"* → Both, layered: gossip for fast membership
  and health; Raft/etcd for the authoritative registry (Consul). Choose Raft when you need strong
  consistency on registration; choose gossip when you can tolerate seconds of staleness and want
  zero operational complexity.

---

### 12.3.11 Failure handling: retries, timeouts, circuit breakers, hedging

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
In a distributed system, **failure is normal and every call will sometimes fail, hang, or be slow.**
The four mechanisms that keep a system healthy under those conditions: **timeouts** (bound the wait),
**retries** (tolerate transient failure), **circuit breakers** (stop wasting calls on a dead
dependency), and **hedging** (fix tail latency).

**Timeouts — the base layer.**
- **Every** network call must have a deadline. No exceptions.
- **Propagate deadlines, not timeouts.** Pass an absolute deadline (`deadline = now + 150ms`)
  downstream. If a hop has only 40 ms left, it should not start a 100 ms DB query — it should fail
  fast or serve degraded. **This single rule eliminates the deepest class of latency bugs.**
- **Do not add timeouts naively** — a timeout that's too tight causes spurious failures that turn
  into retries, which add load, which causes more failures (a **retry storm** / metastable failure).
- **Vary timeouts by operation cost**, not globally: metadata lookup 100 ms, search 1 s, batch
  export 60 s.
- **Timeout ≈ p99.9 of the dependency under normal load** — not the max, not the average.
- **Coordinating timeouts across a chain:** the outer timeout should be the sum of inner budgets plus
  a small margin for overhead, or better, an absolute deadline that each hop respects.

**Retries — mechanics.**
- **Only retry idempotent operations** (or operations made idempotent via idempotency key, 12.3.5).
- **Exponential backoff with jitter**: `delay = random(0, min(cap, base × 2^attempt))`. **Full
  jitter** is the important part — without jitter, all clients retry simultaneously and create a
  thundering herd that never recovers.
- **Cap the total retry time** by the deadline: "retry until the deadline expires", not "retry
  forever".
- **Retry budgets**: cap retries at ~10% of total requests so the system always reserves capacity for
  first-time traffic. (Kubernetes services use this pattern.)
- **Do not retry non-transient errors**: 400, 404, 422, most 4xx. Retry 429 (with Retry-After),
  408, 502/503/504, and connection-level errors.
- **Layer your retries carefully.** Three layers of retries (client + service mesh + SDK) multiply:
  `total attempts = 3³ = 27`. **Pick one layer to retry.** This is a real and very common outage
  cause, and naming it impresses.
- **Beware amplification:** a 1% error rate with 3× retries becomes a 3% load increase on a system
  that is already fragile.

**Circuit breakers.**
- **States:** `CLOSED` (normal) → `OPEN` (fail fast, don't call) → `HALF_OPEN` (let a few probes
  through) → `CLOSED` (if probes succeed) or `OPEN` (if not).
- **Trigger:** failure rate over a rolling window, or a slow-call-rate threshold.
- **Benefit:** you fail fast (milliseconds) instead of queueing (seconds) — this **frees up
  threads/connections** so the rest of the system can serve traffic. **The real win of a circuit
  breaker is that it stops the resource consumption, not that it saves you the error.**
- **Scope per dependency, per call site** — a global breaker is wrong; different operations have
  different failure characteristics.
- **Long-running isolation:** a bulkhead per dependency so a dead service can't consume all
  threads.
- **Interaction with retries:** a breaker without retries is too brittle; retries without a breaker
  hammer a dead service. Use them together: retry *inside* the breaker, and open the breaker only
  after retries are exhausted.

**Hedged requests (tail-latency reduction).**
- **Mechanic:** send the primary request; after the **p95** latency for that endpoint, send a second
  (hedged) request to another server; take the first successful response; **cancel** the loser.
- **Why it works:** if latency is a heavy-tailed distribution, the second request from a
  *different* server has an independent chance of being fast. p99 improves dramatically for ~5–10%
  extra load.
- **Where it's used:** Google's Bigtable hedged requests, Tail at Scale (Dean & Barroso). Every major
  RPC framework has a hedge mode.
- **Cost:** doubles worst-case load during an incident (a bad time to double load) → **only hedge
  below a load threshold**, and cap hedging globally.
- **Related tail fixes:** **request queueing limits** (small queues + fast failures are better than
  huge queues), **connection pre-warming**, **jitter**, and **load balancing by least-outstanding**
  (with small pools, so variance averages out — "least requests" with a pool of 2 has better
  variance than a pool of 32; Hedwig/Finagle documented this).

**Bulkheads.**
Separate thread pools, connection pools, and queues per dependency/feature/tenant so one starving
resource can't take down the whole service. Concrete: `billing-pool (20 threads), search-pool
(10)`, separate DB pools, separate Kafka consumers per rate limit. Also **separate deployments** for
critical vs non-critical paths is the strongest bulkhead.

**Failure detection — heartbeat vs timeout vs lease.**
- **Heartbeat** (active): "I am alive" every N ms. Detects death in N ms. Fails if a process
  freezes (false alive) or the network partitions.
- **Failure detector** (passive, on clients): track error rates/latency of *observed* calls;
  eject a peer after N failures. Doesn't require cooperation; only detects failures you actually
  hit. **Passive detection is more robust in practice** because active heartbeats create false
  positives during GC/network blips.
- **Lease** (cooperative + TTL): safe only with fencing (12.3.4).

**The full toolkit summary.**

| Failure | Symptom | Correct response |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Dependency is slow (not down) | Latency climbing, no errors | Deadline propagation, shed, hedged reads |
| Dependency is down (fast errors) | 5xx, connection refused | Circuit breaker, fast fail, serve stale |
| Dependency is hanging | Timeout storm, thread exhaustion | Timeouts on every call, bulkheads, small queues |
| Retries causing overload | Error rate oscillates, never recovers | Retry budget, one retry layer, jitter, breaker |
| Single node overloaded | Latency outlier, errors from one pod | Outlier detection, eject, LB least-outstanding |
| Thundering herd on a resource | Spike of identical requests | Single-flight, request coalescing, jitter |
| Network partition | Subset can't reach a dependency | Prefer local data, AP/CP choice, fencing |
| Process killed mid-work | Client sees error; work may have committed | Idempotency + retries |

**Common mistakes.**
- No timeouts (thread exhaustion cascade).
- Retries at multiple layers.
- Retrying non-idempotent writes.
- No jitter.
- Circuit breaker opens globally instead of per dependency.
- Huge connection pools (you want *small* pools to limit blast radius and to detect overload fast).
- Not doing **shuffle sharding** / request-level isolation so a noisy tenant can't exhaust the pool.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"A dependency is slow but not down. Your p99 triples. Fix it?"* → Deadline propagation
  (fail downstream fast), circuit breaker with slow-call threshold, bulkhead so its slowness is
  contained, hedged reads to another replica, and shed low-priority work.
- *"What happens if the retry layer and the service mesh both retry?"* → Multiplicative retries
  (3×3 = 9 attempts); during an outage this is a self-inflicted DoS. Fix: one retry layer, a retry
  budget, and a shared token bucket across layers.

---

### 12.3.12 Rate limiting at scale

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Definition.**
Rate limiting caps how much work a caller (user, API key, IP, tenant) can do per unit time. It is
the first line of defense against abuse, runaway clients, and cascading overload, and it must be
**cheap, global, and self-protected**.

**The algorithms.**

| Algorithm | Behavior | Memory | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Fixed window** | Count resets at the boundary | 1 counter | **Boundary burst**: 2× limit across a boundary. Simplest. |
| **Sliding window log** | Keep all timestamps in the window | O(rate × window) | Exact; expensive memory |
| **Sliding window counter** | Weighted previous + current window | 2 counters | Good approximation, cheap — **the practical default** |
| **Token bucket** | Tokens refill at rate R, bucket holds B, each request takes 1 | 1 state | **Allows controlled bursts**; the industry default for APIs |
| **Leaky bucket** | Queue that drains at a fixed rate | queue | Smooths output; can queue or reject on overflow |

**Token bucket, in detail (the one to know cold).**
```
Parameters: rate r (tokens/sec added), capacity b (max burst).
On request: refill = min(b, tokens + r × elapsed); 
            if refill < 1 → reject (429 with Retry-After); 
            else tokens = refill − 1 → allow.
Sizing: capacity b = allowed burst; rate r = your sustainable per-tenant QPS.
```
Token bucket is used by Stripe, AWS, Cloudflare, and Kubernetes API. It **permits bursts up to `b`
while enforcing the long-term average `r`** — that's the property APIs need (a client can burst
after a pause but cannot sustain more than `r`).

**Rate limit granularity — which key?**
- Per user / per API key (fairness and abuse control) — **usually the right primary key**.
- Per IP (anonymous abuse; but NAT breaks it and proxies make it wrong).
- Per tenant/org (fairness across paying customers; protects noisy-neighbor).
- **Hierarchical / multi-dimensional**: check several limits and take the strictest — e.g. per-user
  10/s AND per-org 1000/s AND global 50k/s. This is what mature APIs do.
- **Cost-based**, not request-based: weight expensive operations more (a search costs 10× a read).
  **This is the single most important design idea in rate limiting** — a limit on *requests*
  doesn't control *resource consumption*.

**Distributed rate limiting — the hard part.**
A local in-process limiter can't coordinate across N instances, so the effective global limit is
N × per-instance limit. Options:

| Approach | Latency | Accuracy | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Central store** (Redis `INCR`+`EXPIRE`, or a token-bucket Lua script) | +1 ms | Exact | Redis becomes a critical dependency; **script atomicity is essential** |
| **Token-bucket Lua in Redis** | +1 ms | Exact | Standard approach; do the whole check+decrement atomically server-side |
| **Local limiter with periodic sync** | ~0 | Approximate | Good for soft/global caps; use jittered sync intervals to avoid thundering herd on the store |
| **Quota allocated to instances** | 0 | Conservative | Divide the global quota by instance count; instances self-limit. Fails on autoscaling; needs re-allocation. |
| **Envoy/NGINX local + global** | 0 + occasional | Good | Standard: local token bucket per instance + a global Redis bucket; local for the hot path, global for fairness |
| **Edge/CDN rate limiting** | 0 | Per-PoP | Great for per-IP anonymous traffic; cheap DDoS protection |
| **Hierarchical drift-budget** (Google SRE) | Low | Very good | Client gets a per-request budget; server deducts; client self-throttles. The most sophisticated approach |

**The two-layer design that works in practice:**
1. **Edge/CDN**: per-IP and per-token request rate; absorbs volumetric abuse before it costs you
   anything.
2. **App layer**: **local** token bucket (per instance, no latency) for the hot path, **plus** a
   **global** Redis bucket for tenant/user fairness. Requests must pass both.
This gives you fast rejection for most traffic and correct fairness for the important cases, without
a Redis round trip for every request.

**Rate limiter protection (meta-problem).**
Your rate limiter must not become the outage: cache hot counters, use pipelining, batch
increments, and always fail *open* for internal traffic (or fail closed for security-critical
endpoints — a deliberate choice per endpoint). Also, rate limiting a login endpoint differently
from a search endpoint is normal; and **always rate-limit by API key, not by IP, for
server-to-server calls.**

**Retry-After / client hints.** Return `429` + `Retry-After` (seconds) and/or `RateLimit-Limit`,
`RateLimit-Remaining`, `RateLimit-Reset` headers. Standardizing on the IETF `RateLimit-*` headers
makes your API pleasant and reduces support burden.

**Client-side / proactive:** response headers with a budget (`X-RateLimit-Remaining`) let a
well-behaved SDK back off before being rejected, saving you round trips.

**When to use vs not.**
- Rate-limit every public endpoint; it's cheaper than a DDoS.
- Don't rely on rate limiting to prevent *resource exhaustion* from a single expensive query →
  you need query timeouts, cost-based limits, and per-tenant query budgets.
- For internal (service-to-service) traffic, prefer **load shedding** and **bulkheads** over rate
  limiting, because you don't want a healthy service throttled during a partial outage.

**Trade-offs.**
- Global exact limiting costs a network hop and makes Redis an SPOF; approximate/hierarchical
  methods trade accuracy for latency.
- Rate limiting a paid API can cost revenue; tier limits by plan and communicate them.
- Strict limits protect the system but hurt legitimate bursty users → use token bucket (burst
  tolerance) rather than fixed window.

**Common mistakes.**
- Fixed-window boundary bursts (2× the limit at the minute boundary).
- Not making the check-and-decrement atomic (race → over-admission).
- Rate limiting only requests, not cost → a heavy query still kills you.
- Redis rate limiter with no TTL → memory leak.
- Limiting per IP behind a corporate NAT → legitimate users blocked.
- Not returning `Retry-After`, so clients hammer.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"A single tenant is consuming 60% of your database. Fix it."* → Per-tenant rate limit with a
  token bucket in Redis, per-tenant DB connection pool (bulkhead), per-tenant queue, cost-based
  limits on their expensive queries, and a fair-share scheduler.
- *"Your rate limiter itself fails. What happens?"* → Decide per endpoint: fail open (availability
  for internal traffic) vs fail closed (security for auth/payments); have a local fallback limiter
  so you degrade to approximate rather than none.

---

### 12.3.13 Split-brain, partial failure, and failure modes to be asked about

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
**Split-brain**: two or more nodes each believe they are the authoritative leader/coordinator and
act on it simultaneously. **Partial failure**: the system continues running but with a subset of
its guarantees intact. Knowing the *named* failure modes and their defenses is one of the highest
signal-per-second topics in an interview.

**Split-brain — causes and defenses.**

| Cause | Defense |
| --- | --- |
| Network partition between two halves, each with a majority belief | Consensus with quorum intersection; never elect on a minority |
| Lease/TTL expiry during a pause (GC, VM freeze) | Longer TTL + **fencing tokens** + no process suspension |
| Clock skew makes a lease look valid on two nodes | Don't rely on clocks for safety; fence; or use TrueTime-style bounded error |
| Stale DNS / LB routing sends traffic to both | Fencing; TTL discipline; avoid DNS as the failover mechanism |
| Operator error (two clusters active) | Ownership/quorum service; a single control plane that can demote |
| A "resurrection" bug: a paused node wakes up believing it leads | Fencing; monotonic epochs; **a leader must re-verify leadership before every irreversible action** |
| Two config sources (a stale config service and a local file) | Single source of truth; versioned config |

**The fencing token defense (the answer to give).**
```
Node A: acquires leadership, gets token = 42 from etcd.
Node B: later becomes leader, gets token = 43.
Every write to the shared resource carries its token.
The resource (DB row, object store, external API) tracks the highest token seen
and rejects anything lower.
→ A can never cause damage, regardless of what it believes.
```
This converts a *correctness* problem (must A know it's not the leader?) into an
*availability* problem (worst case the stale write is rejected and retried by the client). That is
a strictly easier problem, and it's the key insight.

**Other named failure modes you should be able to list with a one-line defense.**

| Failure mode | Description | Defense |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Poison pill / poison message** | A message that repeatedly fails processing and blocks a partition | DLQ + skip; per-message error handling; never let one bad message halt a partition |
| **Zombie process** | Dead process still holds a port/lock/resource | Leases with TTL; fencing; heartbeats |
| **Thundering herd** | Many clients retry simultaneously after a recovery | Exponential backoff + **jitter**; **token bucket**; slow-start on new instances; staggered reconnect |
| **Cascading failure** | A slow dependency causes upstream slowdown → more timeouts → more load | **Timeouts everywhere**, **circuit breakers**, **bulkheads**, **load shedding**, **backpressure** |
| **Retry storm** | Retries multiply during an incident | Retry budgets; one retry layer; jitter; backoff |
| **Cache stampede** | Many requests miss the same key simultaneously | Single-flight/lock, request coalescing, **probabilistic early expiration**, jittered TTLs |
| **Cache penetration** | Requests for nonexistent keys | Negative caching; bloom filter; input validation |
| **Cache avalanche** | Whole cache layer expires at once | Jittered TTLs; replicas; warm-up |
| **Gray failure / partial failure** | A subset of requests fail; the rest succeed (e.g. one bad host, one bad shard) | **Outlier detection/ejection**; per-pod health; bulkheads; canaries |
| **Brownout / degradation** | Latency rises but no errors, and load is self-amplifying | Load shedding; queue bounds; deadlines |
| **Retry amplification through queues** | A consumer retries so fast it starves other consumers | Bounded retries + backoff + per-partition ordering |
| **Stale read after failover** | Reads hit a replica that hasn't caught up | Read-your-writes routing; version fences; surface staleness |
| **Backpressure failure** | Producer overwhelms consumer silently until memory dies | Bounded queues; flow control; rejection → backpressure |
| **Clock skew** | TTL logic fires early/late | HLC, TrueTime, generous TTLs, monotonic local clocks |
| **Slowloris / resource exhaustion** | Malicious clients hold connections | Connection limits; timeouts; per-IP limits; load shedding |
| **Long-tail / tail latency amplification** | A 100× p99 in one dependency blows up fan-out latency | Hedged requests, parallel calls, deadline propagation, SLO on the critical path |
| **Unbounded fan-out** | One request triggers 1000 calls | Cap fan-out; batching; bulkhead; async |
| **Deadlock / livelock** | Mutual waiting; retries in a loop | No distributed locks where avoidable; lock ordering; timeouts; jittered retries |
| **Metadata/control-plane outage** | Data plane fine, but coordination (discovery, config) down | Cache config locally; last-known-good; the "control plane must be simpler/more available than the data plane" principle |
| **Data corruption from partial writes** | Torn writes, missing fsync | WAL + fsync before ack; checksums; atomic rename; idempotent replay |
| **Clock skew in rate limiters** | Different nodes think different windows | Use a single store for global limits, or accept approximate |

**The "what could go wrong here?" answer structure — a reusable interview technique.**
```
1. Name the failure        "The cache could serve stale data after a write."
2. Quantify the impact     "Stale for up to the TTL, 60 seconds."
3. Name the blast radius   "All 10k app instances behind one cache tier."
4. Propose detection       "Add a version check + a staleness metric; alert on hit-rate anomalies."
5. Propose mitigation      "Versioned cache keys, or write-through with invalidation."
6. Say what you'd trade    "Staleness for availability — and that's the right trade here
                            because the profile is eventually-consistent anyway."
```

**Partial failure — the honest framing.**
The CAP framing (choose C or A) is too coarse for real incidents. What actually matters is
**which operations remain correct during partial failure**, and you should map that explicitly:
```
During a partition between region A and region B:
  Global login (authn)   → depends on design; if the auth DB is in A only, B users cannot log in.
  Profile read (cached)   → works everywhere (stale). ✅
  Profile read (uncached) → works for A users, fails for B users. ⚠️
  Post creation           → writes to A's home region; B users queue or fail. ❌/⚠️
  Feed read               → works from cache; degraded on new posts. ✅
  Payments                 → fails for B users. ❌ (deliberate: don't take money you can't record)
```
**This table is the real content of "how does your system degrade?" and producing it live is one
of the strongest signals you can send.**

**Design principles that prevent split-brain and partial-failure disasters.**
- **Single writer per key/partition.** Prevents write-write conflicts and most split-brain.
- **Quorum intersection** for any safety decision (2f+1).
- **Fencing** for anything a deposed leader could touch.
- **Idempotency everywhere** so retries after partial failure are safe.
- **Fail-stop assumptions made explicit** — do you assume crash-stop or Byzantine? Byzantine
  requires PBFT-style protocols (3f+1, expensive) and is almost never needed outside of
  financial/multi-party-trust scenarios.
- **Make the control plane more available than the data plane** (Kubernetes, Consul, etcd) —
  otherwise an outage in coordination takes down everything.

**Common mistakes.**
- Saying "we use a leader so there's only one writer" without addressing what happens when the
  leader is partitioned.
- Assuming that because a component "isn't the leader" it will stop serving (it might not know).
- Ignoring **partial** failure of a load-balanced fleet — one bad pod among 50 is the most common
  real incident, and it should be ejected automatically.
- Not having a **game day** / chaos experiment plan.
- No "last known good" fallback for critical paths (a stale-but-serving system beats a correct-but-down one).

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"What happens during a regional network partition in your design, step by step?"* → Walk the
  table above. Name which operations succeed, which fail, and why you chose each.
- *"Your leader is GC-paused for 40 seconds. What happens?"* → The lease expires, a new leader is
  elected, and on resume the old leader still thinks it leads → it must be fenced (or must
  re-verify leadership before any irreversible action). This is *the* concrete version of the
  split-brain question.

---

### 12.3.14 CRDTs awareness

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know (awareness) · 🔴 Advanced (mechanics)

**Definition.**
A **Conflict-free Replicated Data Type** is a data structure whose replicas can be updated
independently, without coordination, and yet **converge deterministically to the same value**
regardless of the order or number of updates. CRDTs are the mechanism behind "AP + correct
outcome," and they are the deepest practical answer to the eventual-consistency problem.

**The three properties (the theory, in one line each).**
- **Commutative:** order doesn't matter.
- **Associative:** grouping doesn't matter.
- **Idempotent:** duplicates don't matter.
If all three hold, replicas converge. **These three properties are the design constraint for every
CRDT.**

**Convergence = commutativity + associativity + idempotency.** The proof: all replicas apply the
same set of operations; because the operation is commutative/associative/idempotent, every replica
computes the same result.

**The important CRDT families.**

| CRDT | Type | How it works | Use |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **G-Counter** | grow-only counter | One counter per replica; read = sum | Distributed counters that never decrement |
| **PN-Counter** | counter | G-Counter + decrement counters, summed | General distributed counter |
| **G-Set / OR-Set** | set | Add elements; OR-Set adds tombstones or unique tags so remove beats concurrent add | Likes, tags, membership (needs care with remove) |
| **LWW-Register** | last-writer-wins value | Keep (timestamp, value); read = max timestamp | "Last position," settings (with HLC timestamps) |
| **MV-Register** | multi-value | Keep a set of concurrent values; the app resolves | When LWW is unacceptable (you must show the user the conflict) |
| **G-Counter (per-key)** | counters | — | Likes, view counts |
| **RGA / Logoot / LSEQ** | **sequence / ordered list** | Per-element unique IDs, causal ordering rules | Collaborative text editing, comment threads |
| **PN-Counter** | counters | — | Views, stock counts (with care) |
| **δ-CRDT / JSON CRDT** | nested JSON document | Merge per field with per-type rules | Config, collaborative documents |
| **Logoot** | ordered list | Distributed counter-based identifiers | Comments, ordering |

**The killer example that makes CRDTs click: collaborative text.**
You cannot use a last-write-wins register on a text document — two people typing would clobber.
Instead: give every character insertion a **unique, causally-ordered ID**; all replicas merge the
set of insertions by that order. Converges regardless of order, and it works offline. This is the
basis of CRDT-based collaborative editors (Automerge, Yjs) and of Figma-like multiplayer design.

**Why CRDTs are *not* a universal answer — be honest.**
- **They don't respect invariants.** A G-Counter can't go negative; an OR-Set can't exceed a capacity;
  a "bank balance ≥ 0" invariant isn't enforceable in an invariant-preserving CRDT unless you add
  an invariant-preserving merge (e.g. `AddWinsMap` / `PN-Counter` with constraints). **You cannot
  have both full availability and arbitrary application invariants.**
- **Semantic merge conflicts.** Two users setting the same field to different values is a
  *business* conflict that no technical merge resolves correctly. Sometimes you need to ask the user.
- **Metadata growth.** Unique tags/tombstones must be garbage collected (with causal stability
  tracking — "safe GC"), which is a real distributed-systems problem in itself.
- **They give eventual consistency.** If your app needs read-your-writes or "no two users ever see
  the same seat sold," a CRDT is the wrong tool — use a strongly consistent store.
- **They're a hammer for the wrong nail.** Most "eventual consistency conflicts" in practice are
  *fine* — the last write wins and users don't care. Reach for CRDTs when the conflict is
  *user-visible and important* (like collaborative editing) or when you genuinely need offline merge.

**CRDT vs OT (operational transformation) vs last-write-wins.**
- **OT** (Google Docs classic): central server orders operations and transforms concurrent ops.
  Requires a central server; it's about *transform* semantics.
- **CRDT**: no central server, converges automatically, merge is commutative/associative/idempotent.
- **LWW**: simplest, works for scalar values, loses concurrent edits.
- **Best practice today:** use OT/CRDT only for genuinely collaborative editing; use LWW/HLC for
  scalar metadata; use strong consistency for money and inventory.

**Practical variants (name them).**
- **Delta-CRDT:** tracks only deltas between versions (efficient for large docs).
- **Interval-CRDT / Fractional indexing (LSEQ, "fractional indexing" as in Notion/Linear):**
  assign ordered identifiers as fractions between neighbors — gives a stable order for a
  user-reorderable list with no coordination. **A very practical, interviewable trick** for
  "user-defined ordering without a transaction."
- **Logoot:** per-character IDs from a distributed counter; scale for large documents.

**When to use vs not.**
- Use: collaborative documents, offline-first mobile apps that merge, collaborative spreadsheets,
  any "no coordination possible" scenario, and CRDT-backed caches for eventual consistency.
- Don't use when you need invariants (money, inventory, uniqueness) or immediate consistency.
- Don't use for single-writer workloads — a plain DB is far simpler.

**Common mistakes.**
- Believing a CRDT means "no conflicts." It means "converges," not "converges to something
  correct for the business."
- Using an LWW register for a text document (data loss).
- Ignoring **safe garbage collection** of CRDT metadata.
- Over-engineering: adopting a CRDT library when LWW-with-HLC would be fine.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How would you build Google Docs / a collaborative canvas?"* → CRDT (or OT) per object,
  operations broadcast over WebSocket with causal metadata, merge on receive, local edits applied
  optimistically (no round trip), presence cursors are ephemeral (gossip), and persistence is a
  snapshot of the merged document.
- *"Offline-first mobile app syncs."* → CRDT or LWW-per-field with HLC; a sync endpoint that takes
  a batch of local ops and returns remote ops since the client's version; idempotent ops so retries
  are safe; conflict UI where LWW would lose user intent.

---

## 12.4 The System Design Framework

**Section goal.** A repeatable, narratable process for a 45-minute design interview. Not a
checklist to recite, but a **spine** that keeps you organized while the interviewer interrupts,
redirects, and asks "why?"

**The framework in one line: Requirements → Estimate → API → High-level design → Deep dives →
Bottlenecks/failure → Trade-offs.** Every step has a purpose, and each has an expected duration.

**Time budget for a 45–60 minute interview.**

| Step | Minutes | Deliverable on the whiteboard |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Requirements & scope | 4–6 | Functional + non-functional bullets, explicit in/out of scope |
| 2. Estimation | 4–7 | QPS, storage, bandwidth, growth — with assumptions written |
| 3. API design | 3–5 | 2–4 endpoints with schema |
| 4. High-level design + data model | 8–10 | Block diagram + data model + schema |
| 5. Deep dives (1–2 areas) | 10–15 | Detailed design of the critical path |
| 6. Bottlenecks, SPOFs, failure | 5–8 | Annotated diagram with failure modes and mitigations |
| 7. Trade-offs, alternatives, monitoring | 4–6 | "We chose X over Y because Z" |

**Pro tip:** the interviewer will tell you where they want to spend time. When you finish
requirements, say: *"I'd like to spend a few minutes on the API and data model, then go deep on
<the interesting part>. Is that a good use of our time?"* This is a **strong** move — it shows
seniority and defuses the most common failure (drawing boxes for 30 minutes with no depth).

---

### 12.4.1 Step 1: requirements, functional and non-functional

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
Requirements are the contract for the design. Functional = **what** the system does. Non-functional =
**how well** it does it (and the constraints on the solution).

**The functional requirement checklist (ask, don't assume).**

```
READS
  ☐ What are the core read paths? (feed, search, detail, timeline, history)
  ☐ What does each read return? (how many items, what fields, personalized?)
  ☐ Pagination: cursor or offset? (cursor is right; offset breaks under inserts)
WRITES
  ☐ What are the write paths? (create, update, delete, like, follow)
  ☐ Are writes synchronous to the user, or can they be async (eventual)?
  ☐ What needs strong consistency? (money, inventory, uniqueness)
  ☐ Soft delete or hard delete? Retention? Legal holds?
RELATIONSHIPS / ENTITIES
  ☐ What are the entities and how do they relate?
  ☐ What is many-to-many? (follows, memberships, permissions)
DISCOVERY / QUERY
  ☐ Search? Filters? Sorting? Facets? Ranking?
  ☐ Any full-text or geo query?
REAL-TIME
  ☐ Notifications? Live updates? Presence? Typing indicators?
  ☐ Delivery guarantees? At-most-once / at-least-once / exactly-once?
MEDIA
  ☐ Uploads? Transforms? Thumbnails? Video? Streaming?
  ☐ Max size, accepted formats, moderation?
AUTH / ACCESS
  ☐ AuthN (who) and AuthZ (what can they do)?
  ☐ Multi-tenancy? Roles? Sharing?
SCALE / SCOPE
  ☐ Users now? In 3 years?
  ☐ Geography? Single region or global?
  ☐ Read:write ratio?
OPERATIONS
  ☐ Who runs it? On-call? Compliance requirements?
```

**The non-functional checklist — where most candidates under-specify.**
```
LATENCY       p50 / p99 targets per key operation. (e.g. feed p99 < 300ms)
AVAILABILITY  99.9% / 99.99%? (see 12.2.5 for cost)
CONSISTENCY   Per-operation: strong / bounded-stale / eventual, with staleness bound
SCALE         QPS now/peak/3-years-out; storage; growth rate
DURABILITY    RPO / RTO (backups, cross-region)
SECURITY      Encryption at rest/in transit, PII handling, audit logs, secret rotation
COMPLIANCE    GDPR/CCPA/HIPAA/PCI; data residency; right-to-erasure
OBSERVABILITY Metrics, logs, traces; SLOs; on-call
COST          Budget ceiling; cost per user; who pays for the extra replicas
REGULATORY/LEGAL Content moderation, copyright (DMCA), age restrictions
```

**Scope control — the most underrated skill.**
Explicitly say what you are **not** designing:
> "I'll scope this to the core photo feed and upload. I'm going to assume a single region, since
> multi-region is mostly a deployment topology on top of this design. Comments, moderation, and
> notifications I'll design at a high level. Let's go deep on the feed read path and upload path."

This gives you permission to be wrong on the unimportant parts and focus on what matters. It also
demonstrates the prioritization a senior/staff engineer has.

**The follow-up questions that signal seniority.**
- "What happens to this design when a user posts something controversial?" (moderation, takedown
  propagation to CDN/cache — an excellent, under-asked question.)
- "How do we delete a user's data across backups, caches, search indexes, and analytics?"
- "What's the rollback plan if we deploy this wrong?"
- "Who is on call for this at 3am, and what does the runbook say?"

**Common mistakes.**
- Starting to draw before asking anything.
- Requirements that are just a restatement of the prompt.
- Not asking about **consistency** or **latency** (the two requirements that change the architecture).
- Forgetting **deletion/retention** (a legal requirement that changes your schema and your CDN).
- Accepting every feature into scope instead of tiering them.
- Not asking about **write patterns** (append-only vs in-place update) — this drives partition design.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Is this system read-heavy?"* → "It depends on the operation: the feed is 100:1 read-heavy, but
  uploads are write-mostly, so I'll design two paths with different characteristics."
- *"Do you need real-time?"* → "Notifications and likes can be eventually consistent within a few
  seconds; the post itself must be visible immediately on the author's read (read-your-writes)."
- *"What about offline users?"* → "Offline sync requires CRDT or last-write-wins with vector clocks
  and a sync endpoint that accepts idempotent operations."

---

### 12.4.2 Step 2: estimation

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
Turn requirements into the four architecture-driving numbers (QPS, storage, bandwidth, growth),
with assumptions visible, in 4–7 minutes.

**What to do, in order.**
```
1. State assumptions in one sentence: "10M DAU, 30% post monthly, read:write 100:1, global,
   3-year horizon, retain photos forever."
2. Compute READ QPS:  DAU × requests/user/day / 86,400 → average → × peak-to-average → × 2
                     headroom. Break it down by endpoint type.
3. Compute WRITE QPS: same, but writes drive a different datastore.
4. Compute STORAGE:    entities × rows × bytes/row (with index overhead) × replicas,
                     PLUS media bytes in object storage (with variants).
5. Compute BANDWIDTH:  QPS × payload size × amplification, for both client-facing and
                     cross-service/cross-region.
6. SANITY CHECK: compare with a known product; if wildly off, re-check assumptions.
7. DECIDE: for each number, say the architectural consequence.  ← the actual point
```

**The numbers to state (template).**
```
"At 10M DAU with 20 requests/day:
  reads  ≈ 2,300 QPS average; global peak ≈ 7,000 QPS; I'll design for 15,000 with headroom.
  writes ≈ 230 QPS average; peak ≈ 1,000.
  Users: 30M rows × 1.5 KB ≈ 45 GB → fits on one Postgres node, sharded later for safety.
  Photos: 1.5M/day × 4.2 MB × 3 years ≈ 6.9 PB → object storage + CDN, not a database.
  Egress: 15,000 QPS × 32 KB ≈ 500 MB/s ≈ 4 Gbps → CDN hit ratio is the cost lever."
```

**Anti-patterns to avoid in this step.**
- Spending more than 7 minutes (it's not the interesting part).
- Computing numbers but never saying what they imply.
- Forgetting peak (the #1 estimation failure).
- Forgetting replicas, indexes, and variants in storage.

**The transition sentence that shows mastery:**
> "These numbers tell me three things: images go to object storage, metadata fits in one relational
> shard for now, and the read path is where all the engineering effort should go. Let me now design
> the API."

---

### 12.4.3 Step 3: API design

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
Define the interfaces at the seams before drawing boxes. 2–4 endpoints is right; 10 is a mistake.
The API design *is* your data model in disguise.

**What to specify for each endpoint.**

```
METHOD + PATH
AUTHN/AUTHZ       who can call it
REQUEST           shape, required/optional, validation, limits (size, count)
RESPONSE          shape, pagination (cursor!), fields, freshness indicator
IDEMPOTENCY       idempotency key support for writes
ERRORS            the error taxonomy (see below) — 400/401/403/404/409/429/503
CONSISTENCY       strong / bounded-stale / eventual — per operation
RATE LIMITS       and what the limit key is
VERSIONING        URL path, header, or compatible evolution
OBSERVABILITY     what you'll log/trace/emit for this call
```

**The endpoints to design in a typical app (choose 2–4).**
```
POST   /users/{id}/photos        upload → presigned URL + finalize
GET    /feed?cursor=&limit=       the hot read path
POST   /messages                 send a message (chat)
GET    /messages?since=          sync messages (cursor-based)
GET    /photos/{id}              detail
POST   /likes                    like (idempotent)
POST   /search                   search query
POST   /users/{id}/follow        follow (idempotent)
GET    /notifications            poll or SSE
```

**Cursor vs offset pagination — know the right answer.**
- **Offset** (`?offset=100&limit=20`): simple, but inserts/deletes shift pages, so users see
  duplicates and misses; deep offsets are O(offset) expensive.
- **Cursor** (`?cursor=<opaque>&limit=20`): stable under mutation, and the DB can seek directly
  (`WHERE (created_at, id) < (?, ?)`). **Use cursor pagination for any feed, log, or
  infinite scroll.** The cursor should be opaque (base64 of the sort key, ideally signed to prevent
  tampering) so you can change the encoding later.
- **Keyset pagination** = cursor pagination done in the DB with a composite index. Say the term.

**The error taxonomy (a mature touch).**
```
400 invalid_request     — malformed, validation failed
401 unauthorized        — not authenticated
403 forbidden           — authenticated but not permitted
404 not_found           — doesn't exist (or 403 to avoid leaking existence)
409 conflict            — concurrent modification, duplicate (idempotency conflict),
                          version mismatch (optimistic concurrency)
410 gone                — resource deleted (good for user-generated content)
413 payload_too_large
422 unprocessable       — semantically invalid (business rule violation)
429 too_many_requests   — with Retry-After
503 unavailable         — overloaded or dependency down; Retry-After set
```
Use **optimistic concurrency** on updates: `If-Match: <etag>` or a `version` field, returning 409
on mismatch. This is the correct answer for "how do you handle two people editing the same
resource" and it comes up constantly.

**Idempotency in the API.**
`POST` endpoints that charge money, reserve resources, or send messages must accept an
`Idempotency-Key` header, and the semantics must be documented: "same key + same body = same
response; same key + different body = 422; in-flight = 409."

**Read-your-writes in the API.**
Return a `version` (or ETag) in write responses and let clients pass it on subsequent reads
(`?min_version=1234`) so the server can guarantee freshness — this is a *deliberate API design
decision* that makes consistency explicit and enforceable, and it shows up in real APIs.

**API evolution.**
- **Additive changes only**: add fields, never remove or rename. Add `deprecated: true` markers.
- **Tolerant readers**: consumers must ignore unknown fields (JSON) — enforce this in your client
  deserializers.
- **Versioning strategies**: URL path (`/v1/...`) is simplest and most visible; header is cleaner
  but invisible in logs; "no versioning, just additive change" works if you discipline consumers.
- **Sunset a version** only after usage telemetry shows zero traffic.

**Common mistakes.**
- Designing 8 endpoints.
- Not specifying pagination (or using offset for a feed).
- Skipping errors entirely (a big differentiator from junior candidates).
- Making the API mirror the database schema (leaks internals, prevents independent evolution).
- Exposing internal IDs and letting clients depend on them.
- Ignoring write consistency semantics in the contract.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How will the client know if its read is stale?"* → Version/ETag + a `X-Data-Freshness`
  header or a `stale: true` flag; or bound staleness via a `min_version` parameter.
- *"Two clients edit the same photo caption. What happens?"* → `version` field + `If-Match`;
  second write gets 409 with the current value so the client can merge or prompt.
- *"How does the client handle a 503?"* → Retry with jitter, respecting `Retry-After`; for
  mutations, retry with the same idempotency key.

---

### 12.4.4 Step 4: high-level design and data model

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
Draw the box-and-arrow diagram and specify the data model. The goal is a coherent sketch, not a
complete design — you will go deeper in step 5.

**How to draw (and what to label).**
```
[Client] → [CDN / Edge LB] → [API Gateway] → [Service A] ──→ [Cache]
                                       └──→ [Service B] ──→ [Primary DB] → [Replicas]
                                       └──→ [Queue] → [Workers] → [Object Storage]
                                       └──→ [Search Index]
```
Label boxes with **the technology you'd actually use** (Postgres, Redis, Kafka, S3, Cassandra) —
this shows real experience, not just boxes and arrows.

**The four-layer pattern that scales.**
```
CLIENT      — web/mobile apps
EDGE        — CDN (static + media), WAF, DDoS protection, TLS termination, geo-routing
APPLICATION — stateless services (the LB → pods tier), API gateway, service mesh
DATA        — relational (transactional), cache, object store (blobs), queue/event bus,
              search index, analytics store
```
Justify the separation: the edge absorbs read traffic; the application tier is stateless and
horizontally scalable; each data store is chosen for a specific access pattern.

**The data model: what to specify.**

For each entity:
```
Table/collection name
Primary key (and why: surrogate vs natural)
Shard key / partition key (and the access pattern it serves)
Foreign keys (and where they're enforced — in the DB or the app)
Uniqueness constraints (and whether they're global or per-shard)
Indexes (and which query each serves)
Expected size, growth, hot/cold strategy
TTL / retention / soft delete
Consistency requirement
```
Plus: **which service owns each table** (in a microservices design, each service owns its data;
cross-service reads go through an API or a read-model/CDC sync).

**Data modeling decisions worth stating aloud.**
- **Relational for transactional/related data** (users, orders, payments, entitlements) because
  you need joins, constraints, and ACID.
- **Object storage for blobs** (images, video, documents) with metadata in the DB.
- **Wide-column/KV for high-volume, key-ordered, append-heavy data** (messages, events, timeline
  entries) partitioned by conversation/tenant.
- **Search index (ES/OpenSearch) as a derived, eventually consistent copy** — never the source of
  truth, never in the write path for correctness.
- **Redis for** hot reads, rate limits, idempotency keys, locks, and counters.
- **Kafka/PubSub for** everything async: fan-out, notifications, analytics, index building,
  cache invalidation.
- **Warehouse/OLAP (BigQuery/Snowflake/ClickHouse)** for analytics on a copy — never query
  production OLTP for reporting.

**The essential data relationships to draw.**
```
User 1—N Post N—1 User          (shard posts by user_id → all of a user's posts are local)
User N—N Post  (likes)           (needs its own table; shard by post_id or user_id — pick one and justify)
Post 1—N Comment                 (shard by post_id)
Conversation 1—N Message         (shard by conversation_id → ordering and fan-out are local)
User 1—N Message (inbox)         (fan-out on write for offline delivery)
```
**Justify the shard key by the dominant query.** This is the single most important data modeling
decision, and the interviewer will test it: "show me the query that this sharding makes fast, and
tell me which query it makes slow."

**Denormalization and read models.**
Say explicitly where you denormalize and how you keep it consistent:
```
Feed        → materialized per-user feed tables, updated asynchronously from events
                 (fan-out-on-read for celebrities, fan-out-on-write for the long tail)
Profile     → denormalized user card in Redis, invalidated on user update events
Counts      → counters maintained asynchronously; approximate is fine (and label it as such)
Search      → ES index built from CDC/Debezium off the primary
```
The interviewer cares that you know the consistency cost of each denormalization.

**Common mistakes.**
- Drawing 15 boxes with no data model.
- Choosing the shard key by "even distribution" instead of by query pattern.
- Putting images in the database (never).
- Making the search index part of the write path.
- Not specifying who owns each table in a multi-service design.
- No consistency story for the denormalized copies (just "we'll update it").

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"You sharded posts by user_id. What breaks?"* → "The 'trending' and 'posts by hashtag' queries
  need a scatter-gather across all shards. I'd fix that with a separate, purpose-built trending
  store (Redis sorted set) and a hashtag index maintained asynchronously."
- *"What if a user's data is in 200 different shards because they have 200 posts?"* → Each post is
  in one shard, keyed by user; a user's posts are co-located *within a shard*, so the count and
  listing are cheap. Show the sharding by explaining the routing, not by confusion.
- *"How do you keep the feed denormalized copy consistent?"* → Events from the primary DB via the
  outbox pattern, an idempotent consumer that updates the feed table, and rebuild/repair tooling.

---

### 12.4.5 Step 5: deep dives into the critical path

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
Pick the **one or two** paths that carry the risk and the load, and design them in real detail:
data flow, algorithm, consistency, and failure behavior. This is where you demonstrate depth.

**How to choose what to deep dive.**
```
Deep dive the hot read path   (the thing that determines cost and latency)
Deep dive the write path      (the thing that determines correctness)
Deep dive the hardest part    (the thing the interviewer hinted at, or the genuinely novel part)
```
Then say so out loud: *"The critical path is the feed read: it's 90% of traffic and it must be
<300ms p99. Let me design that in detail, then cover uploads more briefly."*

**What a deep dive must contain (use this as your internal checklist).**
```
1. The exact read/write flow, step by step, with numbers per hop (latency budget!)
2. Cache design: what's cached, by whom, key, TTL, invalidation, stampede protection
3. Data model for this path: tables, keys, indexes, shard routing
4. Consistency: what can be stale, by how much, and how the API exposes it
5. Concurrency: partitioning/sharding, parallelism, ordering, hot keys
6. Failure behavior: what happens if each dependency is down; degradation ladder
7. Monitoring: the 3–5 metrics that would tell you this path is unhealthy
```

**Example deep dive: the feed read path.**
```
Request GET /feed?cursor=...
1. CDN/LB (5 ms) → API gateway (2 ms) → Feed service (1 ms)
2. Check Redis for the user's cached feed page (1 ms). HIT → return (200 ms total budget spent: 10 ms).
3. MISS → read the materialized feed table for the page of IDs (5 ms).
   - Fan-out-on-read for "followees' recent posts" if the user's feed isn't precomputed.
   - Merge, dedupe, rank (scoring function), and take 50.
4. Batch-fetch the item cards (Redis MGET, one round trip, 2 ms) → avoids the N+1.
5. Missing cards → parallel fetch from the Post service and/or the item cache (5 ms, fan-out
   with a cap of, say, 100 and a 50 ms deadline).
6. Assemble the response; include version/staleness metadata.
Total budget: ~25 ms server-side, leaving room for network + client.

Key design decisions to state:
- The hot path is a *read model*; it never touches the transactional database.
- One MGET for 50 items, not 50 GETs (batch = 100× less network).
- Fan-out is capped and deadline-bounded, so a celebrity with 1M followees doesn't blow the budget.
- Cache stampede protected by single-flight + probabilistic early expiration.
- Staleness: feed is ≤30 s stale; the author's own post appears immediately via read-your-writes
  (their own items are injected at the head of the feed, not read from the cache).
```
**This level of detail — with a latency budget per hop — is what a senior/staff candidate
delivers.** Showing "3 ms + 5 ms + 2 ms" proves you think in latency budgets.

**Latency budget arithmetic (a great thing to volunteer).**
```
SLO: feed p99 < 300 ms end-to-end, p50 < 100 ms.
Budget: network to client ~30 ms | edge+gateway 5 ms | feed service 25 ms |
         item fetch 20 ms | render/other 20 ms  =  100 ms p50 target.
         p99 allowance: 200 ms distributed across the chain, and any single hop
         over budget triggers a degradation (serve partial / cached) rather than a 500.
```

**Deep dive: the write path (upload).**
```
1. Client POST /uploads/init → API returns a presigned S3 PUT URL + upload_id + policy.
   (Server never proxies bytes: saves CPU, egress, and timeouts.)
2. Client PUTs directly to object storage, bypassing your servers.
3. Client POST /uploads/{id}/complete (idempotent).
4. Server: verify object exists + size/checksum, insert metadata row (transactionally),
   emit OutboxEvent (for variants, notification, index, analytics) in the same transaction.
5. Workers consume events: generate thumbnails/AVIF variants → store → update metadata →
   invalidate cache. Each step idempotent.
6. Feed consumers append to followers' feed tables (fan-out-on-write for the long tail,
   fan-out-on-read for high-follower accounts).
Failure handling: incomplete uploads expire (lifecycle rule + a sweeper); duplicate `complete`
   calls are no-ops; variant generation retries with DLQ.
```

**Deep dive: message delivery (chat).**
```
1. Client POST /messages → write to Kafka partition (keyed by conversation_id) → 202 Accepted.
2. Gateway consumers (per conversation partition) deliver to online connections; per-conversation
   ordered delivery via the partition.
3. Offline: async write of an inbox row per recipient; client syncs with `?since=cursor`.
4. Delivery receipts (read cursors) stored per user per conversation; multi-device sync reads
   with the cursor.
5. Idempotency: message id assigned at ingest (client-provided), dedupe on conflict.
6. Ordering: per-conversation sequence from the partition offset; no global order (state it).
7. Presence: ephemeral, gossip-based with TTL; never consistent.
```

**Common mistakes.**
- Deep-diving something unimportant (e.g., the admin API) while the read path stays a box.
- Describing the flow without latencies or numbers.
- Not covering the failure/degradation behavior of the deep-dived path.
- Never getting to depth because you spent all your time on the high-level diagram.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Walk me through this request."* → Do it hop by hop with latencies. Never say "it goes to the
  service and comes back."
- *"What's the p99 of this path and how do you know?"* → From the latency budget + SLOs +
  distributed traces; and here's the metric I'd watch.
- *"How does this path behave when the cache is cold?"* → Stampede protection, warm-up, degraded
  reads, origin load shedding.

---

### 12.4.6 Step 6: bottlenecks, failure modes, SPOFs

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
Now take your own design apart: identify what limits it, what breaks, and what a single failure
kills. This is the step that separates mid-level from senior/staff candidates.

**The bottleneck hunt — the ordered list.**
```
USER-FACING LATENCY limits   → the single slowest dependency in the chain
CPU                          → only if you're actually CPU-bound (check!)
MEMORY / GC                  → allocation rate, cache size
CONNECTION POOLS             → the most common hidden bottleneck (DB, HTTP clients, Redis)
LOCKS / CONTENTION           → row locks, hot rows, distributed locks
DISK IOPS                    → on-disk sorts, checkpoint storms, fsync latency
```
