NETWORK BANDWIDTH            → egress, inter-service traffic, cross-region
SINGLE WRITER                → a shard, a sequence generator, a leader
QUORUM / COORDINATION        → a consensus round trip, a lock service
QUEUE LENGTH                 → unbounded queues turn load into latency
THE ALGORITHM                → O(n²) code, full scans, missing indexes
```
**How to check:** load test to find the knee, look at the metrics that show saturation, use
distributed tracing to find the slowest span, check for lock waits in the DB. **Never assume the
bottleneck — measure it, and say "I'd measure this first."**

**SPOF audit — go component by component.**
```
Load balancer      → 2+ in different AZs; Anycast or client-side fallback
DNS                → don't rely on DNS for failover; multiple resolvers
API gateway        → N+2 instances, autoscaled
Each service       → ≥2 instances across AZs; stateless
Session store      → Redis with replication + eviction policy
Database primary   → SYNCHRONOUS standby in another AZ (this is usually your biggest SPOF)
Database writes    → a single writer per shard is a SPOF for that shard; sharding reduces blast radius
Queue/broker       → replicated (RF 3 across AZs)
Cache              → losing it is a latency event, not an outage (design for it!)
Object storage     → multi-AZ by default; a bucket policy is not an SPOF
Config / secrets   → replicated, versioned, cached locally
Control plane (K8s, etcd) → multi-AZ control plane, ≥3 nodes
IAM / auth         → replicated, cached, with a degraded mode (existing sessions keep working)
Metrics/logging    → losing telemetry must not take down serving
CDN               → losing it degrades to origin traffic; be ready for the load spike
```
Then say: **"My remaining true SPOFs are X and Y; here's the plan for each."**

**Failure modes per component (the table to have ready).**
| Component fails | Detection | Response | User impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One app instance | LB health check / outlier detection | Drain, replace, autoscale | None |
| Cache (Redis) down | Error rate spike | Bypass cache, read from origin, protect origin with shed | Higher latency, higher DB load |
| Primary DB down | Health checks + connection errors | Promote synchronous standby (30–60s), route via proxy | Write errors during failover |
| Read replica down | Lag/error metric | Drop from read pool | None (reduced read capacity) |
| Kafka down | Producer errors | Buffer/retry, degrade (accept write, publish later) | Delayed side effects |
| Object storage down | 5xx | Retry with backoff, block upload completion | Uploads fail, reads of media fail |
| Search index down | Error rate | Fall back to SQL `LIKE`/recent items | Degraded search |
| Dependency slow | Latency spike, circuit breaker opens | Timeout, breaker, degraded response | Partial feature loss |
| Region down | Health checks | DNS/Anycast/client failover; cell activation | ~RTO seconds–minutes of partial outage |
| Bad deploy (code) | Canary + error rate | Automatic rollback | Bounded by canary exposure |
| Bad deploy (config) | Config validation + staged rollout | Rollback config | Bounded |
| Certificate expiry | Expiry monitoring | Automated renewal, multi-cert rotation | Full outage if missed |
| Clock skew | NTP monitoring | Fix NTP; leases sized with slack | Split-brain risk |

**The "what if this dependency is slow vs down" distinction** — always answer both separately.
Slow → timeouts, degradation, deadline propagation. Down → circuit breaker, fast fail, cached
fallback.

**Blast radius reduction (design, not ops).**
- **Cells** — divide the system into N independent cells; a failure affects 1/N.
- **Bulkheads** — per-tenant pools, per-dependency thread pools, separate deployments for
  critical vs non-critical paths.
- **Graceful degradation** — the documented ladder of what to drop first (recommendations,
  personalization, then search, then comments; core feed last).
- **Feature flags** — instant kill switches for any feature, plus config-driven degradation.
- **Timeouts everywhere** — the single biggest cause of cascading failure.

**Common mistakes.**
- Claiming no SPOFs (there are always some; the skill is naming and mitigating them).
- Forgetting the database primary is the most common SPOF.
- Not considering the cache being down (a common real incident).
- Treating a bad deploy as a hypothetical when it's the #1 real cause of outages.
- Talking about failure modes without detection or response.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"The cache is down. Walk me through what happens."* → Everything falls through to the DB; the
  LB sheds to protect it; p99 explodes; the circuit breaker prevents retry storms; then recovery
  (warm-up + stampede protection). Ask for the load math: 15k QPS × 100-byte items × 100 items
  per page is a very different DB load than the "hit" case.
- *"Your primary dies right now. What happens?"* → Failover to synchronous standby in 30–60s;
  writes fail during that window; reads from the promoted primary; any in-flight transactions are
  lost (RPO = 0 with synchronous, but uncommitted transactions fail); then re-establish the old
  primary as a replica.

---

### 12.4.7 Step 7: trade-offs, alternatives considered, rejected options

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
A design is not finished until you can articulate what you chose **against**, and why. This is the
step where you demonstrate judgment. Interviewers are explicitly told to look for it.

**The trade-off sentence formula (use it verbatim).**
> "I chose **X over Y** because **<constraint>**. The cost is **<what X gives up>**, which is
> acceptable here because **<why the product tolerates it>**."

Examples, ready to use:
```
"Chose Redis caching over a read replica for the profile lookup because reads are 95% of traffic and
we need p99 < 50ms; a replica would give us 20ms p99 but with a 2s lag risk and it doesn't reduce
origin CPU as effectively at this QPS. The cost is invalidation complexity, which I handle with
event-driven invalidation and a short TTL."

"Chose Kafka over direct RPC for notification fan-out because fan-out is bursty and a slow
subscriber shouldn't block the write; the cost is eventual delivery and operational complexity."

"Chose Postgres with partitioning over Cassandra for the catalog because we need transactions and
joins, and the catalog fits in memory; the cost is a write throughput ceiling, which I'll hit at
~15k writes/sec and then migrate."

"Chose shard-by-user_id over shard-by-post_id because every query is scoped to a user; the cost is
that global/trending queries need a separate store."

"Chose fan-out-on-read for the feed over fan-out-on-write because celebrity accounts would require
10M inbox writes; the cost is that reading a brand-new user's feed requires a merge at read time."
```

**The alternatives table (have this ready; it's a fantastic artifact to build on the whiteboard).**

| Decision | Options considered | Chosen | Why | Migration path if wrong |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Feed generation | fan-out on read / on write / hybrid | hybrid (write for long tail, read for celebrities) | Balance write amplification vs read latency | Add fan-out-on-write for accounts >10k followers |
| Message store | Postgres / Cassandra / Kafka-only | Kafka + Cassandra | Write throughput + ordering + retention | Start with Postgres, migrate at ~5k writes/sec |
| Cache | Redis single / cluster / none | single cluster with 3 nodes | Simplicity; 100k ops/s is not a problem | Cluster → memcached → CDN |
| Search | ES / SQL LIKE / custom | ES, async from CDC | Relevance + filters + scale | Fallback to SQL for basic search |
| Consistency | strong / eventual / bounded | bounded-stale for reads, strong for writes | Latency + read scaling | Tighten the staleness bound |
| Media | S3+CDN / database blobs | S3 + CDN | Scale, cost, global delivery | — |
| Region | single / active-passive / active-active | single with hot standby (now), cells later | Cost and complexity now | Add cells; the data model supports it |

**The trade-offs you should ALWAYS surface (interviewers expect these).**
```
Consistency vs latency
Read throughput vs write latency
Latency vs cost (redundancy, replicas, regions)
Freshness vs availability (serve stale vs fail)
Cost vs operational complexity (microservices vs monolith)
Flexibility vs simplicity (schema-less vs relational)
Throughput vs latency (batching improves both; sharding improves throughput, hurts coordination)
Strong guarantees vs developer velocity (eventual consistency is easier to build and ship)
```

**"What I'd do differently at 10× the scale"** — a strong closer that shows you think in
trajectories, not snapshots:
> "At 10×, the metadata DB would be the bottleneck. I'd have already partitioned by user_id and
> added read replicas; the next step is moving the feed to a precomputed wide-column store and
> pushing the item cards fully into CDN. The API and sharding key stay identical, so the migration
> is internal."

**Common mistakes.**
- Presenting a design with no alternatives (signals you didn't consider anything).
- Listing alternatives without the reason you rejected them (signals you memorized).
- Claiming a trade-off is free (there is no free lunch; say what you gave up).
- Not having a migration path for the decision you're least sure about (signals you know your
  design's weak point — which interviewers respect).
- Being dogmatic about one technology.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Why not just use X?"* → Have a real answer: the constraint that ruled it out.
- *"What would make you change this design?"* → A named metric (e.g. "if writes exceed 15k/sec
  or p99 crosses 400ms, I'd move the catalog to a partitioned store").
- *"What's the riskiest decision here?"* → Name it honestly (usually the shard key or the
  consistency choice), say why, and say what you'd do to de-risk it (dual-write, shadow reads,
  feature flag).

---

### 12.4.8 Communication: how to narrate, when to draw, being interrupted

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
Design interviews are **communication exercises**. A technically perfect design narrated badly
scores badly. This section is about the mechanics of talking while thinking.

**The narration rules.**
1. **Think out loud, structured.** "So the read path is: client → CDN → gateway → feed service →
   Redis → item cache. Let me start at the client." Structure beats raw stream-of-consciousness.
2. **Announce decisions as decisions.** "I'm going to use Postgres here because…" Not "so, um,
   Postgres."
3. **Use the whiteboard for structure, your voice for reasoning.** Boxes and arrows on the board;
   trade-offs and reasoning out loud.
4. **Name your assumptions as you make them.** "Assuming ~5k writes/sec peak."
5. **Periodically summarize for the interviewer.** "So far we have the write path going through
   Kafka, and the read path served from Redis with a Postgres fallback. Does that match your
   mental model?"
6. **Don't apologize for thinking.** "Let me think for a second" is fine. Silence for 40 seconds is
   not.

**When to draw.**
- Draw the **high-level diagram early** (after requirements + estimation) — it's the shared
  artifact for the rest of the interview. Use it for everything.
- Draw the **data model as a separate small diagram** (entities + relationships), or as a table.
- Draw **timing/sequence detail** for the deep dive (e.g. an ASCII sequence diagram: client →
  gateway → service → cache → DB) — this is much clearer than prose for request flows.
- Draw **failure/timeout annotations directly on the main diagram** in step 6 (timeouts on edges,
  retry counts, breaker states). Annotating the existing diagram beats redrawing.
- **Redraw rarely.** Every redraw costs 3–5 minutes and annoys both of you. Amend and annotate.

**The interruption rule — critical skill.**
Interviewers interrupt to steer you. The correct response:
```
1. STOP talking mid-sentence (do not finish your thought).
2. Acknowledge: "Good question — let me address that first."
3. Answer the question they asked, fully but briefly (30–60 seconds).
4. Explicitly return: "Now, back to the feed read path — where was I? Right, the Redis cache."
```
What not to do:
- Continue your sentence and address the interruption after (it reads as not listening).
- Ignore it because you're mid-flow (this is the fastest way to lose an interviewer).
- Go down a 10-minute rabbit hole answering the interruption (offer to come back to it).
- Get visibly flustered.

**Handling "hold on, let me ask you something else."** → "Absolutely." Then after:
> "That reminds me of something we should handle — <one sentence> — which I'll come back to."

**The clarifying check-ins that signal expertise.**
- After requirements: "Is this the right scope? Anything you'd want to make sure I cover?"
- After the high-level diagram: "Does this look right before I go deeper?"
- Before deep diving: "I'd like to go deep on <X> — is that where you'd like to spend time?"
- At the end: "Is there a part of this you'd like me to go back and stress-test?"

**Time management.**
- Keep a mental clock; if you're at 25 minutes with no deep dive, stop the high-level diagram.
- It's fine to say: "I want to make sure I go deep on <X> — let me move faster here."
- It's fine to *ask* for time: "I have about 15 minutes left. Let me cover failure modes and
  trade-offs, which I think are the most valuable remaining topics."

**What to do with silence.** If the interviewer goes quiet, they're evaluating. Fill it with a
self-directed question: "Let me stress-test this design for a failure case — what happens if the
cache is down?" Silence is often a signal to keep talking.

**Presenting the conclusion.** End with a compact summary:
> "So the design is: CDN + stateless API tier; Postgres partitioned by user for metadata;
> object storage + CDN for media; Kafka for async; Redis for the feed read path; strong
> consistency only for payments and idempotency; bounded staleness for everything else. The
  biggest risks are the shard key and the feed cache stampede, and here's how I de-risk both."

**Common mistakes.**
- Talking continuously without structure (hard to follow, impossible to interrupt).
- Drawing a giant diagram before doing any estimation.
- Answering an interruption with a paragraph that ignores the actual question.
- Silently thinking for a minute.
- Not summarizing or checking in.
- Ignoring the clock and running out of time before the deep dive.

**Interviewer probes / self-checks.**
- They may go silent (evaluation mode) → keep talking with self-directed analysis.
- They may ask "what are you thinking?" → narrate your decision process explicitly.
- They may say "you don't need to worry about X" → accept immediately, don't re-litigate.
- They may say "let's go deeper on Y" → drop everything and go. Flexibility is the point.

---

### 12.4.9 Handling questions you cannot answer

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Definition.**
You will be asked about technologies and internals you don't know. **How you handle not knowing is
graded.** Panic is the only fatal answer.

**The framework: acknowledge → reason → bound → offer.**

```
Step 1 — ACKNOWLEDGE (immediately, without hedging into fiction)
  "I haven't worked with Cassandra's internals directly."
  "I don't know the exact implementation of that."
  Do NOT bluff. Interviewers can tell, and a correct-sounding bluff that collapses under one
  follow-up question is much worse than an honest gap.

Step 2 — REASON from what you DO know (this is where you score)
  "What I do know is the access pattern: ordered writes at high throughput per partition,
   with time-series reads. That's the same shape as a log-structured store, which suggests
   an LSM-tree design with a memtable and compaction. I'd guess they use something like
   that, but I'd want to read up on it."

Step 3 — BOUND the uncertainty and state what you'd do
  "For the design, what matters is the guarantees: I need per-partition ordering and
   a bounded staleness on reads, and I'd pick the tool based on that. I'd validate by
   running the load test."

Step 4 — OFFER a concrete follow-through
  "I can go deeper on the consistency model and partitioning, which I'm confident about,
   or I can tell you what I'd need to learn to answer that."
```

**Templated responses for common gaps.**

*"I don't know X" (a technology):*
> "I haven't used X in production. Based on its guarantees I would expect it to be a fit here
> because <reasoning from the access pattern>. If I needed to use it, I'd start with the
> documented consistency model and load-test the write path. I'd rather not guess at the
> internals — do you want me to focus on the part I do know?"

*"I don't know the number" (an estimate):*
> "I don't remember the exact figure, but it's in the low tens of milliseconds for that class of
> operation in-region; I'll design the timeout with a 2–3× margin and verify with a measurement."

*"I don't know the algorithm":*
> "I know the problem and the guarantees I need; let me reason about the approach." → Then think
> out loud. Interviewers often care more about your reasoning process than the recalled answer.

*"I don't know at all, and it's the core of the question":*
> "This is outside what I know. Let me tell you how I'd approach it: <framework>. Can I ask you
> one clarifying question so I can reason about it? <question>"

**Never do these.**
- Bluff and hope. Follow-up questions are designed to catch bluffs.
- Say "it depends" without saying on what.
- Apologize repeatedly ("sorry, sorry, I don't know, that's embarrassing"). Say it once, move on.
- Get defensive or argue with the interviewer's premise.
- Change the subject abruptly in a way that dodges the question.

**The recovery when you realize you bluffed.**
> "Let me correct what I said — I overstated that; I know the general approach but not that
> specific detail. The part I'm confident about is <X>."

Recovering gracefully is itself a positive signal. Doubling down is a negative one.

**The meta-principle.** The interview is not testing whether you know everything; it is testing
whether you can **reason** about unfamiliar systems, admit limits, and make sound decisions under
incomplete information. **Explicitly naming what you don't know, while showing how you'd reason
about it, is a senior behavior.**

**Common mistakes.**
- Long silence while trying to remember.
- Inventing specifics (fake benchmark numbers, fake API names).
- Treating "I don't know" as a catastrophic failure that derails the rest of the interview (it
  doesn't — keep going and recover).
- Not redirecting to what you *can* contribute.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Do you know X?"* → Honest answer, then reasoning. If they push: "no, but here's how I'd
  figure it out."
- *"What's your familiarity with Y?"* → Calibrate honestly: "I've used it in a side project and read
  about the design; I've never operated it at scale."

---

### 12.4.10 The scoring rubric interviewers actually use

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Definition.**
Design interviews are scored against an explicit rubric. Knowing it lets you allocate your time to
what is actually being measured. Most companies use something close to Google's published rubric
and Meta's "product design" rubric.

**The common rubric (scaled 1–4; 1 is well below, 2 is at/above bar for the level, 3 is clearly
above, 4 is exceptional).**

| Dimension | What "below bar" looks like | What "clearly above" looks like |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Requirements & scope** | No clarifying questions; builds everything or the wrong thing | Asks about scale, consistency, latency, deletion, ops; explicitly scopes in/out |
| **Estimation** | No numbers, or numbers with no architectural consequence | Derives numbers, states assumptions, peak-to-average, and links each to a decision |
| **API / interface design** | No API, or an API that mirrors internals | Clean contracts, pagination, error taxonomy, idempotency, consistency semantics |
| **Data model** | Vague "we'll store posts"; no keys | Concrete entities, keys, shard keys justified by query, indexes, ownership |
| **High-level design** | Boxes and arrows with hand-waving; monolith boxes; no technology | Layered, coherent, realistic components; caching and async paths visible |
| **Deep dive / depth** | Stays at the surface; no latency accounting; nothing hard | Follows the critical path in detail with numbers, budgets, and edge cases |
| **Scalability & bottlenecks** | "We'll scale it horizontally"; no specifics | Names the actual bottleneck, quantifies it, has a plan and a migration path |
| **Reliability & failure** | "It'll be highly available"; no SPOF analysis | Timeouts/retries/breakers/bulkheads/shedding; SPOF list; degradation ladder |
| **Trade-offs & alternatives** | One design, no alternatives | Named trade-offs, rejected options with reasons, 10× trajectory |
| **Communication** | Monologue, unstructured, hard to interrupt; silent thinking | Structured narration, uses the board, listens and pivots, summarizes |
| **Handling unknowns** | Bluffing, or "I don't know" + dead air | Honest, reasoned, redirects to strengths, recovers |
| **Product / user thinking** | Purely technical; ignores failure UX | Considers degraded UX, moderation, deletion, abuse, growth of features |

**How the levels actually differ** (this is the most useful part).

**Mid-level (3–5 yrs) — expected:**
- Clarifies scope; does basic estimation; designs a straightforward architecture (LB → service →
  DB + cache); identifies a couple of SPOFs; names caching and load balancing; some trade-off
  discussion; clear communication.
- **Not expected:** deep consensus knowledge; subtle failure analysis; multi-region design;
  novel algorithm design.
- **Common failure:** tries to look senior and stumbles on basics. Fix: nail fundamentals.

**Senior (5–8 yrs) — expected:**
- Proactively covers consistency, peak traffic, failure modes, and the read/write path asymmetry.
- Makes *justified* shard-key and consistency choices and defends them.
- Mentions timeouts, retries, circuit breakers, idempotency, caching strategy, and monitoring
  unprompted.
- Has a 10× trajectory and a migration path off the riskiest decision.
- Handles interruption well and summarizes.

**Staff/Principal (8+ yrs) — expected:**
- **Reframes the problem**: identifies the *real* business constraint (cost per user? moderation
  liability? data residency?) and optimizes for it, not for technical elegance.
- **Reasons about the whole system**: multiple regions, cells, cost, org structure, on-call, and
  the evolution of the design over years.
- **Discusses operational reality** as a first-class concern: deploys, backfills, schema
  migrations, incident response, capacity planning.
- **Makes trade-offs at the business level**: "this costs $X/month more, which is Y% of our
  margin per user; I'd do it because Z."
- **Names the risks honestly** and proposes experiments to de-risk them (shadow reads, canaries,
  dual-writes).
- **Simplifies**: a staff engineer's design has *fewer* moving parts than a mid-level's, with
  better-justified choices.
- **Drives the discussion**: proposes the agenda, asks the interviewer what they care about,
  keeps the interview on the highest-value topics.

**What interviewers explicitly look for as signals.**
```
+ Asks about non-functional requirements before designing.
+ Says "how stale is acceptable?" without being asked.
+ Mentions idempotency keys for money operations.
+ Mentions timeouts and circuit breakers for every external dependency.
+ Distinguishes average from peak; includes headroom.
+ Says "images go in object storage, not the database" unprompted.
+ Mentions read-your-writes when introducing replicas.
+ Mentions a migration path from the design you're least confident in.
+ Names a bottleneck and quantifies it.
+ Has an SLO/error-budget thought.
+ Mentions monitoring/metrics before being asked.
+ Pushes back politely on an unrealistic requirement ("if we need p99 <100ms globally and
  strong writes, that's a contradiction — here's the option set").
+ Says "I don't know, here's how I'd reason about it."
```

**What gets a candidate downgraded.**
```
− No clarifying questions at all.
− Estimation with no architectural consequence ("so we need 500 GB").
− Only one storage technology ("everything in Postgres") with no justification.
− No failure analysis ("what happens if this service is down?").
− Ignoring peak traffic entirely.
− Bluffing when asked about internals.
− Talking continuously without letting the interviewer steer.
− Designing 20 boxes with no depth anywhere.
− No acknowledgment of cost, ops burden, or trade-offs at a senior level.
− Ignoring deletion, moderation, or abuse at a senior level (product thinking).
− Insisting on a tech choice when a constraint rules it out.
```

**The self-assessment checklist before you finish an interview.**
```
□ Did I clarify requirements and scope before designing?
□ Did I estimate QPS (avg + peak), storage (incl. replicas/indexes), and bandwidth?
□ Did every estimate have a stated architectural consequence?
□ Did I design an API with pagination, errors, and idempotency?
□ Did I pick a shard key and justify it with a query pattern?
□ Did I go deep on at least one path with latency numbers?
□ Did I identify the bottleneck and quantify it?
□ Did I list SPOFs and say how I'd remove each?
□ Did I discuss timeouts, retries, breakers, shedding?
□ Did I name at least 3 trade-offs and rejected alternatives?
□ Did I discuss observability and the 10× trajectory?
□ Did I handle the unknown question honestly?
```

**How to prepare for the level you're targeting.** Interview the rubric, not the topic list:
- **Mid:** get fundamentals bulletproof (databases, caching, load balancing, queues, estimation).
- **Senior:** add failure modes, consistency, and trade-offs to every answer.
- **Staff/Principal:** add business constraints, multi-region, cost, org design, and simplification.

---

# 12.1-12.4 ESTIMATION DRILLS (15)

> Instructions: for each drill, write down your answer **first**, then read the model reasoning.
> The point of these is not to memorize numbers — it's to build a habit of *stating assumptions
> out loud and attaching every number to an architectural decision*. Time yourself: 3–5 minutes
> each, like the real thing.

---

## Drill 1 — Photo app: read/write QPS

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Question.** A photo-sharing app has 10M DAU. Each user views 200 photos per day (in feed
pages of 50, so 4 feed page loads per day) and uploads 0.2 photos per day on average. Give read
QPS and write QPS, average and peak, and state the design headroom.

**Model reasoning.**
```
ASSUMPTIONS (state these aloud):
  - 10M DAU
  - Feed page = 50 items → 4 feed loads/user/day (already given by "200 photos/day")
  - 1 feed load = 1 API request that internally fans out (see note)
  - Upload rate given: 0.2/user/day
  - Peak-to-average for a global product: ~3×
  - Design headroom: 2× peak

READ:
  Reads/day     = 10M users × 4 pages = 4 × 10^7 page loads/day
  Average RPS   = 4 × 10^7 / 86,400 ≈ 460 QPS
  Peak RPS      = 460 × 3 ≈ 1,400 QPS
  Design target = 1,400 × 2 ≈ 2,800 read QPS at the edge

  ⚠ The trap: "200 photos/day" is not 200 API calls. It's 4 calls returning 50 items each.
    If the implementation is 50 separate calls per page, it's 200 calls/user/day =
    2×10^7/day = 231 QPS avg, 700 peak — and that's a *worse* architecture, not a smaller system.
    Always ask: how many round trips?

WRITE:
  Writes/day    = 10M × 0.2 = 2 × 10^6 photos/day
  Average WPS   = 2 × 10^6 / 86,400 ≈ 23 writes/sec
  Peak WPS      = 23 × 3 ≈ 70; with a viral event, design for ~500

DECISIONS (the actual answer):
  - Read path is 40× the write path → read replicas + aggressive caching are worth it;
    write path can stay on a single writer for a long time.
  - 2,800 read QPS: ~10 app instances at ~300 RPS each with 2× headroom. Trivial.
  - The system is NOT compute-bound. It's storage- and bandwidth-bound (see Drill 3).
```
**The lesson.** Distinguish *items served* from *requests*. Interviewers listen for it.

---

## Drill 2 — Photo app: storage

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Question.** Same app: 10M DAU, 0.2 photos/user/day. Photos are stored forever in 4 variants
(original 3 MB, large 1 MB, medium 200 KB, thumbnail 20 KB). Metadata row is ~1 KB raw. Compute
5-year storage and tell me where each part lives.

**Model reasoning.**
```
Writes/day  = 2 × 10^6 photos/day
5-year total = 2 × 10^6 × 365 × 5 ≈ 3.65 × 10^9 photos

IMAGES (object storage):
  Per photo = 3 MB + 1 MB + 0.2 MB + 0.02 MB = 4.22 MB
  Total     = 3.65 × 10^9 × 4.22 MB ≈ 15.4 PB
  → S3 + CDN. NOT a database. Ever.
  → Lifecycle: originals to Glacier after 90 days (cost drops ~70%)
  → Variants are content-addressed (key = hash) so they're immutable and cacheable forever

METADATA (database):
  Rows       = 3.65 × 10^9 rows
  Raw bytes  = 3.65 × 10^9 × 1 KB ≈ 3.65 TB
  With indexes + MVCC + fill factor (≈2.5×) ≈ 9 TB
  With 1 primary + 2 read replicas = 3 copies ≈ 27 TB
  → Fits comfortably on ONE modern server (8–16 TB NVMe).
    So: single shard now, but partition by (user_id, created_at) from day one so sharding
    later is a config change, not a migration.
  ⚠ Also: 3.65B rows in Postgres means ALTER TABLE is a multi-hour operation with a full
    table rewrite. That alone is the reason to partition.

DECISIONS:
  - Images: object storage, 15 PB, CDN-fronted. Hot set (30% touched per month) in CDN.
  - Metadata: partitioned relational. Shard key = user_id (co-locates a user's photos;
    the dominant query is "my photos" and "photos of my followees").
  - Cost reality: at $18/TB/mo, 15 PB ≈ $270k/mo before CDN egress. The CDN hit ratio and
    the variant sizes are the two biggest levers. Say this — cost is a design input.
```
**The lesson.** Always multiply for index/replica overhead, and never put blobs in a database.

---

## Drill 3 — Photo app: bandwidth and CDN economics

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Question.** The feed API returns 50 items; each item's JSON is ~600 B. Thumbnails are 20 KB
each. 2,800 peak read QPS, 95% CDN hit ratio. Compute user-facing bandwidth, origin egress, and
monthly cost. Then: how would you halve the bill?

**Model reasoning.**
```
USER-FACING (this is what the user's ISP sees; the hard constraint):
  JSON per page   = 50 × 600 B = 30 KB
  Thumbs per page = 50 × 20 KB = 1 MB
  Per request     = 1.03 MB
  Peak to users   = 2,800 × 1.03 MB ≈ 2.9 GB/s ≈ 23 Gbps
  ⚠ On a 4G phone (≈10 Mbps) that's ~0.9 seconds just for data. Mobile payload size is a
    product constraint, not just a cost one.

ORIGIN EGRESS (what you pay for):
  With 95% CDN hit ratio, 5% reaches origin:
    Origin egress = 2,800 × 1.03 MB × 0.05 ≈ 144 MB/s ≈ 1.15 Gbps
  Month: 144 MB/s × 2.6M s ≈ 375 TB/month
  At $0.08/GB ≈ $30k/month

COST LEVERS (ranked by impact):
  1. CDN hit ratio 95% → 99% cuts origin egress by 60% (to ~60 TB/mo, ~$15k/mo).
     Why: signed URLs, correct Cache-Control, versioned URLs, no Vary explosion, avoid
     Set-Cookie on cacheable responses.
  2. Smaller payloads: 50 → 20 items per page (2.5× less); request 2 pages of 25 in parallel
     only if the UX needs it. AVIF thumbnails at 12 KB instead of 20 KB = 40% less.
  3. Brotli/gzip on JSON: 30 KB → 6 KB. (Thumbnails are already compressed; don't waste CPU.)
  4. Progressive rendering: send 10 items first, then the rest. Cuts time-to-first-item
     dramatically even if total bytes are unchanged.
  5. Cache item cards in Redis so even a 99% CDN miss is a 1 ms read.

DECISION TO STATE: "CDN hit ratio and item count per page are my two biggest cost levers, and I
would treat them as SLOs (≥99% hit ratio on item assets)."
```
**The lesson.** Compute origin *and* user bandwidth; name the levers ranked by impact.

---

## Drill 4 — URL shortener / link shortener

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Question.** Design the estimation for a URL shortener: 100M links created total, 5M DAU
creating 1 link/day, each link clicked 5 times/day on average, 200 links/user/day for heavy
users. Read-heavy or write-heavy?

**Model reasoning.**
```
WRITES:
  5M/day → average 58 writes/sec; peak ~180; headroom ~500. Trivial.

READS (redirects):
  5M links × 5 clicks = 25M clicks/day
  Average = 25M / 86,400 ≈ 290 reads/sec
  Peak    = 290 × 4 ≈ 1,200 reads/sec (peak-to-average is higher for click traffic,
            because of diurnal web usage) → design ~3,000

  ⚠ The key insight: read:write is 5:1 — NOT the 100:1 of a feed app. Link shorteners are
    comparatively write-heavy and the *hot set* problem dominates, not throughput.

STORAGE:
  5M/day × 365 × 5 = 9.1B links
  Row = (id, long_url ~200 B, short_code, user_id, created_at, expires_at) ≈ 300 B
  Raw ≈ 2.7 TB; with indexes ≈ 8 TB; with 2 replicas ≈ 24 TB → fits on one node, shard when
  it doesn't.  No partitioning needed unless you add expiry-by-time (then partition by month).

BANDWIDTH:
  Redirect = 301/302 + tiny body ≈ 500 B
  3,000 QPS × 500 B ≈ 1.5 MB/s. Negligible.
  ⚠ But the *external* hop to the destination is the user's problem, not yours. The user
    experiences: your latency (5–50 ms) + DNS for your domain + the destination's latency.

DECISIONS:
  - Cache aggressively: short_code → long_url in Redis. Redirects are THE hot path.
  - Hot links: a viral link can be 1M QPS on one key → per-key cache in memory at the edge
    (CDN or L7 cache) so you never touch Redis for it. This is the classic hot-key problem.
  - Never do a DB lookup for a redirect: cache-first (or a 301 with long Cache-Control so
    browsers and CDNs cache the redirect itself).
  - Short code: base62 of a counter (collision-free, needs a sequence) vs hash+collision
    retry. Counter + base62 is simpler and gives shorter codes.
```
**The lesson.** Recognize the hot-key pattern; redirects must be served from cache, never the DB.

---

## Drill 5 — Chat app: write QPS and storage

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Question.** Chat app: 10M DAU, 50 messages/user/day average. Compute write QPS (ingest),
delivery QPS, and 1-year storage. Is this read-heavy or write-heavy?

**Model reasoning.**
```
INGEST (writes):
  5×10^8 messages/day → average 5,800 msg/sec; peak ×4 ≈ 23,000; headroom 2× → ~50,000.

  ⚠ This is the biggest realization in the drill: at 10M DAU with normal usage, chat is a
    WRITE-heavy system. Engineers routinely assume chat is read-heavy because "people read
    more than they write" — but every message is *delivered* (read) and delivery is the
    expensive part.

DELIVERY (reads):
  Assume average 3 devices per user receiving each message:
    5,800 × 3 ≈ 17,400 delivery events/sec average
    Peak ≈ 70,000/sec
  This is over long-lived WebSockets → the gateway fleet must hold ~100k+ concurrent
  connections with heartbeats and backpressure.

STORAGE:
  1.8×10^11 messages/year × ~800 B (row + index) ≈ 145 TB/year
  With 2 replicas ≈ 435 TB. Partition by conversation_id, time-bucket by day.
  ⚠ Ordered by conversation → partition by conversation, NOT by user (a user's messages span
    many conversations). Fan-out to inboxes by user is a separate write.

READ:WRITE RATIO:
  By message count: 1:1 for storage (each message is written once).
  By delivered read: ~1:3.  → NOT read-heavy.  Storage design must prioritize WRITE throughput.

DECISIONS:
  - Kafka as the ingest buffer (partitioned by conversation_id → ordering for free).
  - Wide-column store (Cassandra/DynamoDB) partitioned by conversation, time-bucketed.
  - Fan-out on READ for online delivery; fan-out on WRITE to inboxes only for offline users.
  - Postgres is viable up to ~5k writes/sec; at 23k peak, migrate. Abstract the store behind
    an interface from day one so the migration is a config change.
```
**The lesson.** Re-derive "read-heavy vs write-heavy" from numbers, not intuition. Chat is
write-heavy at the storage layer.

---

## Drill 6 — Rate-limited API: what QPS must the edge handle?

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Question.** B2B API: 2,000 enterprise customers. Each has a per-customer rate limit of 100
requests/sec (token bucket, burst 200). What is the peak aggregate QPS the edge must handle?
How many edge nodes?

**Model reasoning.**
```
AGGREGATE LIMIT:
  2,000 customers × 100 rps = 200,000 QPS maximum sustained (if all customers max out)
  With token bucket burst 200 → instantaneous peak can be 200,000 + burst.

REALITY CHECK (this is the good part):
  Not all customers max out. Assume 10% peak concurrency factor (typical for a
  well-behaved B2B population):
    Realistic peak ≈ 20,000 QPS; the 200,000 is the worst case the *limit system* must
    survive without breaking.

EDGE NODES:
  20,000 QPS: an L7 proxy node does ~5,000–50,000 RPS depending on TLS termination,
  payload size, and ruleset complexity. Conservative: 5,000 RPS/node → 4 nodes for realistic
  peak, 40 nodes for the absolute worst case.
  ⚠ Don't size for the worst case at the edge. Size for realistic peak and rely on
    per-customer limits + a global limit + load shedding for the pathological case.

DOWNSTREAM:
  20,000 QPS to the origin tier → at 500 RPS/node, 40 nodes.
  Origin DB: 20,000 QPS × 1% write = 200 writes/sec (fine), but 20,000 reads/sec must be
  served by cache (95% hit) → 1,000 QPS to the DB (needs replicas).

DECISIONS:
  - Rate limiting is enforced at the edge (per API key), with the global budget enforced
    centrally (Redis token bucket) for fairness across edge nodes.
  - Because limits are per-customer, one customer's spike cannot starve others → per-customer
    bulkheads at the origin (separate connection pools or a fair-share scheduler).
  - 429 + Retry-After + standard RateLimit-* headers.
```
**The lesson.** Distinguish the *limit* from the *realistic load*; size for reality and shed for
the pathology.

---

## Drill 7 — TinyURL: hot key / viral link

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Question.** A short link gets 500,000 clicks in 10 minutes. Your design has a Redis cache in
front of Postgres. What happens, and what do you do?

**Model reasoning.**
```
NUMBERS:
  500,000 clicks / 600 s ≈ 830 QPS on ONE key.
  ⚠ If it's a celebrity post, that could be 100,000 QPS on one key.

WHAT HAPPENS (naive design):
  - All 830 QPS hit the same Redis key. Redis is single-threaded per shard but 830 ops/s is
    fine — Redis handles 100k+ ops/s. So Redis alone is OK.
  - The real danger is the **cache stampede on expiry**: if the TTL is 3600s and it expires,
    all 830 QPS miss simultaneously → 830 concurrent Postgres queries on one row. Postgres
    handles that but it's 830 connections and lock contention on one row.

FIXES (in order):
  1. Put a **CDN/L7 cache in front** with a long TTL. A redirect response is tiny and identical
     for everyone → the edge absorbs 95%+ and Redis/Postgres see almost nothing.
     This is the correct primary answer: **the hottest thing in your system should be served
     from the edge, not the origin.**
  2. **Never expire hot keys**: refresh the TTL asynchronously (a "keep-alive" job) so the hot
     key never misses. Classic technique.
  3. **Single-flight** in the app: concurrent misses for the same key collapse into one load.
  4. **Probabilistic early expiration** (`exp - 30s*rand()%1000`) to desynchronize expiry.
  5. If it exceeds one machine: **replicate the hot key across N cache nodes** (a "hot key
     cache tier") chosen by a second-level hash.

DECISION TO STATE: "Redirects are cacheable and immutable. I put them in the CDN with a long TTL
and use signed URLs only if I need per-customer analytics, which I don't for redirects. So a viral
link costs me CDN bandwidth only — and I keep a keep-alive so the origin never sees the spike."
```
**The lesson.** Push the hottest read to the edge; never let a hot key expire under load.

---

## Drill 8 — Video streaming: storage and bandwidth

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Question.** A video platform stores 5-minute videos at ~50 MB each (already transcoded to one
adaptive bitrate). 10M DAU, 3% upload daily, average user watches 40 min/day. Compute storage
per year, and origin bandwidth if 90% is served from CDN.

**Model reasoning.**
```
STORAGE:
  Uploads/day = 10M × 0.03 = 300,000 videos/day
  Per year   = 300,000 × 365 ≈ 110M videos
  Bytes      = 110M × 50 MB ≈ 5.5 PB/year
  → Object storage. This is the cheap part.

WATCHING (bandwidth):
  Total watch time/day = 10M × 40 min = 4×10^8 minutes/day
  Bytes = 4×10^8 min × 50 MB/5min = 4×10^8 × 10 MB/min = 4 × 10^9 MB = 4 PB/day
  ⚠ 4 PB/day = ~46 MB/s average... let me recompute: 4 PB/day / 86,400 s ≈ 46 GB/s ≈ 370 Gbps
  average. Peak (×4) ≈ 185 GB/s ≈ 1.5 Tbps.

  Cost at $0.05/GB egress = 4 PB/day × 30 × $0.05 ≈ $6M/month. ⚠⚠ THIS is the real cost.
  → Video streaming is a *bandwidth* business, not a storage business.

  With a 90% CDN hit ratio, origin egress is 10% → ~$600k/month. Still large.
  → The #1 lever is the CDN hit ratio. #2 is adaptive bitrate (ABR) so mobile users get
    2 Mbps streams, not 10 Mbps. #3 is prefetch/shorten watch windows.

DECISIONS:
  - Transcoding is the expensive *compute* line item: 300k videos/day × (multi-resolution
    transcode ~30s of CPU per video per rendition) → dedicated autoscaling worker fleet with
    a queue. Budget this explicitly; it's the hidden cost people forget.
  - Video in HLS/DASH with 3–5 renditions; segments content-addressed; manifest short TTL.
  - Origin shielding + tiered cache so a new release isn't a stampede.
  - Watch-position writes are 10M/day (tiny) but must be async + eventually consistent.
```
**The lesson.** For media businesses, bandwidth and transcode compute dominate storage. Always
compute the *watch* side, not just the *upload* side.

---

## Drill 9 — Global app: region split

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Question.** Same photo app, but now: 40% of users in the US, 30% in EU, 30% in APAC.
Global users, p99 latency target <200ms. Single region or multi?

**Model reasoning.**
```
GEO SPLIT:
  US 40% of 10M DAU = 4M    EU 30% = 3M    APAC 30% = 3M

SINGLE REGION (say US-East):
  - Users in EU: ~100 ms RTT added to every request. APAC: ~180 ms.
  - p99 <200ms is IMPOSSIBLE for APAC users from a single US region (network alone eats it).
  → Conclusion: single region fails the latency SLO. This is a physics argument, not a
    capacity argument, and it's the strongest form of the multi-region case.

MULTI-REGION (active-active, 3 regions):
  - Local latency everywhere (<30 ms). ✅
  - But writes: if strong consistency is global, every write pays ~100-200 ms extra. With a
    global latency budget of 200 ms, a globally-synchronous write is unaffordable.
  → Therefore: home-region per entity (write locally, replicate async), and accept
    cross-region staleness.

CONSISTENCY PLAN:
  - Profile/photo writes: local to the user's home region (strong within the region).
  - Feed reads: local, bounded stale ≤30s. Feed generation is per-region.
  - Follow graph: this is the hard part. A follow graph is inherently cross-region.
    Options: (a) replicate async, accept follower counts vary by region for seconds;
             (b) global social graph service (expensive, high QPS);
             (c) store follows in the *follower's* home region and make fan-out region-aware.
    ⚠ Name this as the hard problem — it's exactly where the interviewer is testing you.
  - Likes: AP globally (per-user-region write, per-region counter, merge).
  - Payments/money: home-region only, strongly consistent. Never cross-region.
  - ID allocation: a global ID service (Snowflake-style: time + node + sequence) so IDs are
    unique without a global lock.

FAILOVER:
  - Anycast or client-driven failover. DNS failover is too slow (TTLs).
  - Cache warming in the failover region, else you get an origin stampede.

DECISION TO STATE: "3 active-active cells, home-region per entity, async replication, strong
consistency only within a region and only for money. Data residency: EU user data stays in the EU
cell (GDPR)."
```
**The lesson.** Latency SLOs force multi-region; then consistency must be relaxed. Name the
cross-region hard problem (the social graph) explicitly.

---

## Drill 10 — IoT / telemetry ingestion

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Question.** 10M devices send a telemetry reading every 60 seconds. Reading is 200 bytes.
Compute ingest QPS, storage/year, and tell me the three architectural consequences.

**Model reasoning.**
```
INGEST:
  10M devices / 60 s = ~167,000 messages/sec average, 24/7 (no diurnal variation!)
  Peak ≈ 1.5× for reconnect storms ≈ 250,000/sec
  ⚠ Reconnect storms after an outage are the real spike: all 10M devices reconnect within
    minutes → 10M messages arrive in a 60-second window = 167k/s sustained for a minute,
    plus TLS handshakes. Design for this.

VOLUME:
  167,000/s × 200 B = 33 MB/s = 33,400 GB/day ≈ 12 PB/year raw
  ⚠ With replication (RF 3): 36 PB/year. With compression (protocol buffers + zstd, ~4×):
    ~3 PB/year logical → ~9 PB with replication.

THREE ARCHITECTURAL CONSEQUENCES (state all three):
  1. You cannot query this from the database. It goes to a **time-series or wide-column store**
     (TimescaleDB, InfluxDB, ClickHouse, Bigtable, or object storage + Athena) with
     time-based partitioning + retention/rollup. OLTP Postgres is a non-starter.
  2. You must **aggregate at ingest** if you can. Devices that send "temp=20.1°C" 1,440×/day
     are redundant. If the API allows, send rollups (min/max/avg per minute) → 24× less volume.
     Even if you can't, aggregate in the stream layer: "store raw for 7 days, 1-min rollups
     for 90 days, 1-hour rollups for 2 years" reduces storage by ~50×.
  3. **Cost per device is the business constraint**: $20/TB/mo × 12 PB = $240k/month for raw.
     With rollups, maybe $20k/month. At 10M devices, that's $0.002/device/month vs $0.024 —
     a 10× difference in unit economics. Rollup policy is a *business* decision.
  Bonus 4: **backpressure** — devices must tolerate loss. Buffer on device (store-and-forward),
     batch uploads (60 readings in one request = 60× fewer requests and 1 RTT instead of 60).

LOSS TOLERANCE:
  Telemetry is the canonical "loss is fine" workload → CAP theorem says go AP. Accept dropped
  readings; never block on backpressure for a 200-byte heartbeat.
```
**The lesson.** Telemetry forces AP + aggregation + time-based storage, and cost per device is the
business framing.

---

## Drill 11 — Search-backed product (e-commerce)

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Question.** E-commerce: 5M products, 200M orders/year, 50M DAU. Each user does 4 search
queries/day and 1 product page view per 3 search results. Compute search QPS, catalog read QPS,
and design implication for search.

**Model reasoning.**
```
SEARCH:
  4 queries × 50M DAU = 2×10^8 queries/day
  Average = 2,300 QPS; peak (×3, global) ≈ 7,000; design 15,000

PRODUCT PAGE VIEWS:
  Search results → 1 product view per 3 results. But p99 searches have many results and
  many users abandon. Assume 3 product views per search (upper bound of engagement):
  50M × 4 × 3 = 6×10^8 views/day
  Average = 6,900 QPS; peak ≈ 21,000; design 42,000

CATALOG DATA:
  5M products × ~10 KB (title, description, attributes, images refs, price history) = 50 GB
  → Small. Fits in memory. This is the key fact for search design.

IMPLICATIONS:
  1. The catalog is SMALL (50 GB) but HOT. **Search should be memory-resident**: an in-memory
     inverted index (Elasticsearch, Vespa, or a custom index) with 2–3 nodes holding the whole
     index. This is a rare case where "just put it all in RAM" is the correct answer, and
     saying so (with the size math) is a strong signal.
  2. Search is a **derived index** — never the source of truth. Built from the product DB via
     CDC (Debezium) or events. Consistency: seconds behind. Name this.
  3. Product detail pages: serve from CDN + Redis, not from a search query. Price and
     inventory are fetched live (they must be current), everything else is cached.
  4. **Price/inventory correctness**: search result shows cached price (fine, seconds stale);
     the checkout path re-reads price and inventory from the source of truth (strong). This
     is exactly the "consistency per operation" answer.

ORDERS (write path):
  200M orders/year = 550 orders/sec average; peak ~2,000.
  → Postgres sharded by user_id, or a single Postgres with partitioning will handle this fine
    for years. Don't shard the orders table prematurely.
```
**The lesson.** Sometimes the whole dataset fits in memory — compute the size and say so.

---

## Drill 12 — Notification fan-out

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Question.** 10M DAU, average user has 200 followers, 1 post/day by 10% of users. Compute the
fan-out event rate. Design implication?

**Model reasoning.**
```
POSTS:
  10% of 10M = 1M posts/day

FAN-OUT:
  Average followers = 200 (⚠ but the distribution is power-law: median might be 20, and a
  few users have 10M followers. Average is a trap here.)
  Naive: 1M posts × 200 followers = 2×10^8 fan-out events/day
  Average = 2,300 events/sec; peak ×4 ≈ 9,000/sec

  Real distribution matters:
  - 99% of users have <200 followers → fan-out-on-write is cheap for them
  - 0.01% (1,000 users) have >100k followers → fan-out-on-write for those = 1000 × 100k =
    10^8 events in one burst. ⚠ A single celebrity post = 100M writes. This is the
    classic viral-write problem.

DECISION — HYBRID FAN-OUT:
  Threshold = 1,000 followers:
  - Below threshold: fan-out on write. Write an inbox row per follower (in a sharded store),
    asynchronously via Kafka. Reads are trivial (read your inbox).
  - Above threshold: fan-out on read. Do NOT write 10M inbox rows. When a celebrity's
    follower reads their feed, merge the celebrity's recent posts at read time.

  Why the threshold: fan-out-on-write has cost O(followers) per post; fan-out-on-read has
  cost O(followers read) but you only pay it for accounts people actually visit. For 1,000
  accounts, read-time fan-out is much cheaper overall.

  Implementation of read-time fan-out: fetch the celebrity's last N posts (one query),
  merge with the precomputed feed by time, take the top K. Cap the fetch (e.g. 20 posts) so
  latency is bounded.

ALTERNATIVE: pull-based feed (Twitter-style) — no fan-out at all, timeline is a
reverse-chronological index of followees' posts (a Redis sorted set per user, updated on
post). Cost = O(1) per post. But ranking and relevance get harder.

⚠ Observability: alert on "posts by accounts > threshold that triggered fan-out" so you can
  catch viral events before they hurt.
```
**The lesson.** Fan-out is O(followers); use a hybrid threshold. Power-law means averages lie.

---

## Drill 13 — Multi-tenant SaaS: per-tenant resource estimation

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Question.** Multi-tenant B2B SaaS, 50,000 tenants, wildly different sizes. Top 1% of tenants
(500) generate 60% of traffic; median tenant generates 1 QPS. Design implication?

**Model reasoning.**
```
TRAFFIC:
  Median tenant: 1 QPS. 500 large tenants: if they generate 60% of total, and total is
  50,000 QPS → large tenants do 30,000 QPS / 500 = 60 QPS each (but these are likely much
  higher — assume a few hundred each).
  Long tail: 49,500 tenants × 1 QPS ≈ 50,000 QPS total.

NOISY NEIGHBOR IS THE PROBLEM:
  A single tenant at 60× the median can saturate shared pools: DB connections, thread pools,
  CPU, cache, rate limits. This is the #1 failure mode of multi-tenant systems.

DESIGN IMPLICATIONS (all four):
  1. PER-TENANT RESOURCE ISOLATION. Separate connection pool per tenant tier, or a fair-share
     scheduler (weighted round robin across tenants, not FIFO). A tenant queue that can be
     starved is better than a tenant that can starve others.
  2. PER-TENANT RATE LIMITS + QUOTAS (12.3.12), and per-tenant cost-based limits (a tenant
     running 1000 heavy queries must be limited, not a tenant running 1000 light ones).
  3. TENANT SHARDING BY TIER. Large tenants on dedicated infrastructure (or a dedicated
     shard); small tenants share. Shard key = tenant_id. This is the standard SaaS answer.
  4. SHARD KEY = tenant_id. It co-locates all of a tenant's data (simple queries, easy
     per-tenant backup/restore/GDPR deletion, easy per-tenant encryption keys). Downside:
     skew (a huge tenant is a hot shard) → mitigate with the tiering above.

PER-TENANT OPERATIONS (often forgotten but real):
  - Per-tenant backup & restore (compliance requirement)
  - Per-tenant GDPR erasure (delete only that tenant's rows across all tables, indexes,
    caches, search indexes, analytics — and object storage)
  - Per-tenant rate limiting and cost attribution (show in your bill!)
  - Per-tenant audit log
  - Per-tenant encryption key (for high-security tiers)

⚠ Hot shard detection: alert on per-shard QPS and per-tenant QPS; if one tenant saturates a
  shard, split it (tenant-id sharding with a directory lets you split a tenant across shards).
```
**The lesson.** Multi-tenant = isolation + tiering + per-tenant operations. Shard by tenant_id and
say why.

---

## Drill 14 — Scaling a hot table: when to partition, when to shard

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Question.** Your `events` table has 8B rows, 60 GB, growing 200M rows/day. Queries: "all
events for user X in the last 90 days" (99% of traffic) and one ad-hoc analytics query per
day over all time. Partition or shard? What key? What happens in 2 years?

**Model reasoning.**
```
CURRENT: 8B rows, 60 GB — ⚠ this FITS IN MEMORY on a 2 TB machine. Volume is NOT the reason
  to shard. This is the most important realization and most candidates miss it.

QUERY PATTERNS:
  Hot (99%): SELECT ... WHERE user_id = ? AND ts > now() - 90d   → 90-day window
  Cold (rare): full-scan analytics, once/day, ad hoc

PARTITION BY (user_id, month)? 
  ✗ Combining user_id and month means 10M users × 120 months = 1.2B partitions. Absurd.
  
PARTITION BY RANGE(ts), SHARD BY HASH(user_id) — the correct two-level answer:
  - 120 monthly range partitions (manageable, enables retention/rollup, prunes the 90-day
    window to 3 partitions per shard)
  - HASH(user_id) across N shards → even write distribution and even read distribution
    (a user's events are on exactly one shard)
  - Each (shard, month) is a table. Total physical tables = N × 120. With N=16, that's 1,920
    tables. Postgres handles that; beyond a few thousand it needs care.

  Read path: "user X, last 90 days" → 1 shard, 3 partitions, index scan. Fast. ✅
  Cold analytics: run against a warehouse fed by CDC, never against OLTP. ✅

WRITE PATH:
  200M rows/day = 2,300 writes/sec average — fine for Postgres.
  Growth: in 2 years, +146B rows, +1.1 TB. Still one machine is possible but IOPS will be the
  limit, not capacity.

WHAT BREAKS FIRST: not storage — **index depth and write amplification**. B+ tree with 8B+
  entries = 4–5 levels, so every insert costs more page splits; MVCC bloat grows; autovacuum
  falls behind; CHECKPOINT/VACUUM windows grow; ALTER becomes impossible.

DECISION:
  1. Partition by month NOW (before it hurts — retrofitting partitioning on 8B rows is a
     multi-week online operation).
  2. Shard by user_id when write QPS or index size demands it (make it a config parameter).
  3. Move analytics to ClickHouse/BigQuery via CDC.
  4. Retention: keep hot data 90 days in OLTP; roll up and archive older data.
  ⚠ Say this explicitly: "I partitioned because of the 90-day access window, not because of size."
     That's the insight that earns the point.
```
**The lesson.** Partition for *access patterns* and retention, not size. Shard only when write
throughput or index size truly demands it. Composite partitioning mistakes are common.

---

## Drill 15 — The 10× trajectory: what breaks, in order

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Question.** Your photo app works at 1M DAU. Someone asks what breaks at 10M DAU. Walk through
in order.

**Model reasoning.**
```
ASSUMPTIONS: 10× everything (users, photos, reads, writes), same per-user behavior, same
99.99% SLO, same p99.

STEP 1 — WHAT BREAKS IMMEDIATELY?
  - Image storage: 0.44 PB → 4.4 PB. Fits in S3 (no problem). Cost: $8k → $80k/month. ⚠ Cost
    becomes the first real constraint, not capacity.
  - Metadata DB: 260 GB → 2.6 TB raw, ~26 TB with 3 copies. ⚠ NOW it needs sharding. This is
    the first *technical* break. Fix: shard by user_id (16–32 shards, directory-based).
  - Read QPS: 460 → 4,600 avg, 14k peak. ⚠ The app tier needs ~50 instances and the cache tier
    becomes critical (Redis at 14k ops/s × ~50 items/page = 700k item reads/sec — ⚠ this is
    the real bottleneck! Batching + cache hierarchy now mandatory).

STEP 2 — WHAT BREAKS NEXT?
  - The item-card cache at 700k reads/sec is not free. Fix: (a) MGET batching (done),
    (b) CDN caching of item JSON (bigger win), (c) a two-level cache (L1 in-process + L2 Redis),
    (d) co-located caches (hash item_id to the same app instance) so a page load is mostly
    local.
  - Feed generation: materialized feed tables per user (100k+ users × 500 items) becomes a
    storage problem (100M+ rows) and an invalidation problem. Fix: hybrid fan-out, cap
    materialized depth, TTL.
  - Search: Elasticsearch index of 3.6B items (photos + captions). ⚠ Reindexing takes days;
    snapshots and blue/green indices become necessary. Fix: index sharding, incremental
    indexing, versioned indices.

STEP 3 — WHAT BREAKS LAST (but breaks)?
  - The metadata DB write path: 230 writes/sec avg. Still fine with 32 shards.
  - Postgres maintenance: 3.6B metadata rows across shards is fine, but backups of 26 TB take
    hours. Fix: incremental backups, PITR tuning, and accepting longer RTO.
  - Search index storage: 3.6B docs × 1 KB = 3.6 TB — needs sharding and careful mapping.
  - Ops: 50 app instances + 32 DB shards + cache clusters + ES cluster = hundreds of
    machines. ⚠ **Operational complexity now dominates.** This is the step where microservices
    and cells start to pay for themselves.

STEP 4 — WHAT DOESN'T BREAK (and should be defended)?
  - S3 + CDN for images: scales to millions of PB. Never needs redesign.
  - Object storage doesn't need sharding by us.
  - The API surface doesn't change.
  - The shard key (user_id) doesn't change — this is why choosing it well mattered.

THE TRAJECTORY LESSON:
  "Capacity problems come first and are solvable with money. Correctness problems (wrong shard
  key, wrong consistency) come first in *decision* order and are not solvable later. So the
  decisions I need to get right now, before scaling, are the shard key, the consistency model,
  and the cache key design. Everything else can be bought."
```
**The lesson.** Distinguish "what breaks" (solvable with money) from "what you must get right
now" (decisions you can't reverse). Naming that distinction is a staff-level insight.

---

# 12.1-12.4 FAILURE-MODE DRILLS (12)

> Format: a scenario, then the **structured failure analysis**. Practice naming the failure,
> quantifying the impact, giving the detection, the mitigation, and the trade-off you accept.
> Full reasoning shown for each.

---

## Drill F1 — The cache is down

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Scenario.** Your feed service is 15,000 read QPS with a Redis cache at a 97% hit rate. Redis
becomes unavailable for 4 minutes.

**Analysis.**
```
WHAT HAPPENS:
  Miss rate goes 3% → 100%. Origin DB load = 15,000 QPS (vs 450 normally) = ⚠ 33× increase.
  Worse: every request becomes a multi-item fetch (50 items per page) → if uncached,
  15,000 × 50 = 750,000 item reads/sec against the DB. This does not merely slow down — it
  will time out, and then retries make it worse.

WHY IT'S WORSE THAN IT LOOKS:
  Cache-aside means a cold cache is a *stampede* by construction. Plus connection pools
  exhaust, so even successful queries can't get a connection.

CASCADE (the real damage):
  DB saturation → slow queries → thread pool exhaustion → p99 goes from 80ms to 30s →
  clients time out → clients retry → MORE load on a system that's already dead →
  circuit breakers open → 503s → retry with jitter → recovery is slow.

WHAT YOU SHOULD DO (in order, and fast):
  1. Circuit breaker on Redis → fail fast, stop retry storms.
  2. **Bypass the cache** and serve from origin — but with a **global rate limit / shed**
     (12.1.9): accept only ~2,000 QPS, shed the rest with 503 + Retry-After. This is the
     key move: you deliberately trade availability for staying alive.
  3. Serve **stale data** if you have a local in-process LRU cache (bounded, e.g. 60 s TTL).
     In-process caches survive Redis being down — this is a real reason to keep them.
  4. Disable non-essential features (recommendations, personalization) to reduce queries.
  5. Protect the DB: connection pool cap (PgBouncer), statement timeout, read-only mode
     for read replicas only, reject writes if needed.

RECOVERY:
  - Don't warm the cache eagerly at full rate; it will stampede again. Warm gradually.
  - Add jittered TTLs so a whole keyspace doesn't expire together next time.
  - Enable single-flight and probabilistic early expiration so this is bounded next time.

PREVENTION (what to build *before* the incident):
  - Redis Cluster or Sentinel with automatic failover (or a second cache tier).
  - Local in-process cache (Caffeine) in front of Redis — survives Redis outages, absorbs
    maybe 40% of traffic on its own.
  - CDN caching of item JSON so the origin sees far less traffic in the first place.
  - Origin load shedding configured and tested (not just written).
  - Alert: cache hit rate drop >20% for 2 min → page.
```
**The lesson.** A cache outage is a *multiplier* event, not a 1× event. Design the miss path
before the hit path.

---

## Drill F2 — Database primary failover

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Scenario.** Your primary Postgres crashes (host loss) at 3pm. You have a synchronous standby
in another AZ. Feed QPS is 15,000; writes are 800/sec.

**Analysis.**
```
WHAT HAPPENS, MINUTE BY MINUTE:
  t=0s    Host dies. Connections break. In-flight transactions are lost (⚠ RPO: uncommitted
          writes; with synchronous replication, *acknowledged* writes are safe).
  t=0-30s  The HA agent / Patroni / cloud RDS detects failure and promotes the standby.
          ⚠ During this window, ALL WRITES FAIL (30-60s is typical). Reads also fail unless
          they're already on a different replica.
  t=30-60s New primary is live. Connection poolers reconnect (⚠ pool warm-up: existing pooled
          connections are dead, so there's a thundering-herd of reconnects; PgBouncer helps).
  t=60s+   Steady state. The old primary comes back and is rebuilt as a replica
          (⚠ pg_rewind/base backup required — cannot just rejoin).
  LAG:     The new primary was synchronous to within 0 transactions, so no data loss from
          acknowledged writes. But ⚠ it was NOT the failover target for reads; replicas may
          have been laggy → read staleness during and after.

WHAT THE USERS EXPERIENCE:
  - ~30-60s of write failures (uploads, posts, likes fail).
  - ⚠ Elevated errors during failover: 5xx on writes, and p99 spikes on reads from replicas
    that are catching up.
  - ⚠ Idempotency matters enormously here: clients retrying after failover must not
    double-write. This is why the idempotency key record must be committed *atomically*
    with the write (12.3.5).

WHAT TO DO WHEN IT HAPPENS:
  1. Verify it's a real failure, not a network partition (⚠ split-brain risk: if the old
     primary is alive but partitioned, you now have two primaries). Use fencing / a witness.
  2. Promote. Don't wait for the automatic agent if it's stuck (and know how).
  3. Verify the new primary: is it writable? Do replicas follow it?
  4. Shed: if the DB is degraded after promotion, cut non-essential reads/writes at the edge.
  5. Communicate: status page. 30-60s of write errors is a customer-visible event.
  6. Rebuild the old primary as a replica; never demote it back to primary casually.

HOW TO PREVENT / REDUCE IMPACT:
  - Synchronous replication (RPO=0 for acknowledged writes) — costs ~1 RTT of write latency
    (1-2 ms in-region) but eliminates data loss. ⚠ Trade-off: you take on the latency cost
    permanently to avoid a rare loss. Usually worth it for money.
  - Multi-AZ with automated failover (managed) or Patroni + etcd for self-managed.
  - ⚠ To reduce the 30-60s window: keepalive + fast failure detection (e.g. postgres
    `wal_sender_timeout`, HA agent heartbeat ~3-5s). Some systems get failover to <10s.
  - ⚠ To reduce *impact*: make writes retryable with idempotency keys so the 30-60s window
    is a latency blip for clients instead of errors.
  - Connection pooler (PgBouncer) so the app reconnects in milliseconds, not by rebuilding pools.
  - **Test failover regularly** (game days). Untested failover fails.

INTERVIEWER ANGLE: "What's your RPO and RTO, and what makes them those numbers?"
  RPO = 0 (synchronous standby). RTO = 30-60s (detection + promotion).
```
**The lesson.** Failover is not instantaneous, and it's not free. Name the 30-60s window and
make writes retryable so the window is survivable.

---

## Drill F3 — A bad deploy

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Scenario.** You deploy a change that causes an N+1 query on the feed endpoint. 100% of instances
roll out. Feed QPS is 15,000. Latency goes from 80ms p99 to 12s.

**Analysis.**
```
WHAT HAPPENS:
  1. Rolling deploy reaches 100% of instances (no canary).
  2. Each request now does ~50 extra DB queries (N+1: fetching item cards one-by-one).
  3. DB load: 15,000 × 50 = 750,000 QPS against a DB sized for ~50,000.
  4. DB saturates. Latency explodes (queueing: past the knee, latency is exponential).
  5. Timeouts start. Retries begin. ⚠ Retry amplification makes it worse.
  6. Connection pools exhaust → entire app, not just the feed, degrades.
  7. Cache becomes useless (every request also pays the DB cost).

CASCADE SCOPE: This is a *global* outage caused by one endpoint's inefficiency, because they
  share a database. ⚠ Shared-database coupling means a local bug becomes a global outage.

WHY 12s: queueing. As ρ→1, response time ~ 1/(1-ρ). At ρ=0.99, latency is 100× the service
  time. This is why "the query only got 10× slower" becomes "the system is down."

THE FIX, MINUTE BY MINUTE:
  1. **Roll back immediately** (don't debug in production; the change is suspect by definition).
     ⚠ But confirm the metric is actually caused by the deploy before rolling back.
  2. Shed: disable the feed endpoint or serve cached/stale responses while you roll back.
  3. Let DB connections drain; the pool may need a restart if exhausted.
  4. Watch for secondary damage: replicas may have fallen behind, cache may have been
     poisoned by timeouts, some side effects (metrics, alerts) may be lost.
  5. Post-incident: root cause (N+1 in code review / missing performance test).

HOW TO PREVENT (the real lesson):
  - **Canary deploys** (1% → 10% → 50% → 100%) with automatic rollback on error-rate/latency
    SLO violation. ⚠ This would have limited the blast radius to 1% of traffic.
  - **Feature flags** — gate the change so you can turn it off without a deploy.
  - **Automated rollback** on SLO breach, not on manual decision (humans are slow at 3am).
  - **Load tests in CI** (k6/LOCUST) — an N+1 in a hot endpoint should fail the build.
  - **Query count budgets** — a canary metric: "p99 DB queries per request." ⚠ N+1 is visible
    as a spike in *query count* even before latency moves.
  - **DB circuit breaker** so a hot endpoint can't exhaust the shared pool. Bulkhead the
    feed's DB access separately from other endpoints.
  - **Separate read pools per endpoint** so feed load can't starve checkout.

⚠ Also worth noting: the N+1 might have passed code review because tests used small datasets.
  This is why performance characteristics need their own tests.
```
**The lesson.** Deploy safety (canary + auto-rollback + feature flags + bulkheads) is a
*design* concern, not a process concern. The N+1 became a global outage because of shared
resources.

---

## Drill F4 — Network partition between two services

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Scenario.** The Order service (region A) and the Payment service (region B) cannot reach each
other for 10 minutes. Partitions and heals cleanly.

**Analysis.**
```
CLASSIFY FIRST (this is the key skill):
  - Is it a partition, or is one service actually down? (Check both sides' perspective.)
  - Is it symmetric (both can't reach each other) or asymmetric (one-way)?
  - Is it a full partition or only some traffic (e.g. only large payloads)?
  ⚠ Asymmetric partitions (A can't reach B, but B can reach A) are the nightmare case for
    split-brain detection.

IF ORDERS USE 2PC / DISTRIBUTED TRANSACTIONS (the bad design):
  ⚠ The transaction coordinator is BLOCKED. Inventory holds are locked. Orders are stuck
  in-flight. Nothing progresses. Users see "order pending" forever.
  Impact: full outage of checkout, plus stuck state that requires manual cleanup after.
  This is exactly why 2PC is avoided.

IF ORDERS USE A SAGA (orchestration) — the right design:
  1. Order create succeeds (local), status = PENDING.
  2. Payment step fails (timeout) → retry with backoff, bounded by the step deadline.
  3. After the deadline → COMPENSATE: cancel the order, release inventory holds.
  4. The user gets "order could not be completed" — a clear failure, not a stuck state.
  5. ⚠ Compensation calls also fail during the partition → they retry with backoff → the
     saga is flagged for manual intervention if compensation fails.
  The right outcome: **no stuck state, no locked resources, clear user feedback, and a durable
  record of what happened.**

IF ORDERS ARE AP-DESIGNED (async, payment is eventually consistent):
  1. Order accepted, payment pending.
  2. An outbox/event delivers the payment request when connectivity returns (durable, so no
     loss).
  3. ⚠ RISK: the physical product could ship before payment completes → **financial loss**.
     Mitigation: hold shipment until payment is confirmed (a saga step / state machine gate).
     "Never do an irreversible step (ship) before a required confirmation (paid)."

KEY QUESTIONS TO ASK AS THE ENGINEER:
  - Where are the durable records of in-flight operations? (Saga store / outbox table.)
  - What is the timeout/retry budget for each step?
  - What is the compensation, and is it idempotent?
  - How does a user learn what happened? (status endpoint, notification, UI state)
  - What's the manual repair path?

SPLIT-BRAIN CHECK:
  - Is there any state where BOTH sides think they own the same resource? (E.g., a scheduler
    that runs in both regions.) ⚠ The scheduler must use a lease or fenced lock (12.3.4).

COMMUNICATION: This is a user-visible degradation. Status page + support tooling. The
  "orders pending" count is the key metric to watch during and after.
```
**The lesson.** Partitions are survivable if (a) you never block on a 2PC, (b) every operation
has a durable state, (c) compensation exists and is idempotent, and (d) irreversible steps are
gated on confirmations.

---

## Drill F5 — Slow dependency (not down)

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Scenario.** The Recommendation service's p99 goes from 30ms to 4 seconds. It's not down — it's
*slow*. Your feed endpoint calls it synchronously.

**Analysis.**
```
WHAT HAPPENS (cascade, as designed by your timeouts — or lack of them):
  1. Feed requests wait 4s for recommendations.
  2. Each waiting request holds a thread/connection from the feed service's pool.
  3. At 15,000 QPS × 4s = ⚠ 60,000 concurrent in-flight requests.
  4. Feed service's thread pool (say 200 threads) is exhausted in milliseconds.
  5. All other endpoints sharing that pool (profile, likes) also fail. ⚠ One slow dependency,
     whole service down. This is a cascading failure.
  6. Clients time out and retry → load increases → worse.

THIS IS THE MOST IMPORTANT FAILURE MODE IN MICROSERVICES (the "chatty synchronous call chain").

THE FIXES, IN ORDER OF POWER:
  1. **Timeout on the recommendation call** (e.g., 100ms) + deadline propagation. The
     recommendation result is optional, so on timeout just return the feed without it.
     ⚠ The feed serves fine without recommendations — that's the whole point of a
     deadline.
  2. **Make the dependency async** (the right design): fan out recommendations to a queue,
     merge results asynchronously (eventual personalization), or precompute recommendations
     offline (they don't need to be real-time).
  3. **Circuit breaker** on the recommendation dependency (open on slow-call rate, not just
     error rate). Fail fast in ~0ms instead of waiting for timeouts.
  4. **Bulkhead**: separate thread pool + connection pool for recommendations (e.g., 10
     threads) so its slowness is contained. Feed still has threads to serve the core path.
  5. **Load shedding for low-priority work**: when the circuit is half-open or the pool is
     saturated, skip recommendations entirely (feature flag toggled by system health).
  6. **Hedged requests** (if you must call it): after p95, fire a second call to a different
     replica, take the first success.

FAILURE-DETECTOR CHOICE (a subtle, high-value point):
  - Fixed timeout (e.g., 500ms) is brittle: too tight = false failures, too loose = slow failure.
  - Adaptive/percentile-based timeout (e.g., 3× the rolling p99, bounded) adapts to load. ⚠ But
    adaptive timeouts can collapse under load (when everything is slow, the timeout grows →
    even slower). Use bounded adaptive + a hard maximum.

WHAT YOU'D DO RIGHT NOW:
  - Open the circuit breaker on recommendations. Feed p99 returns to 80ms in seconds.
  - Verify: is the DB or thread pool still recovering? Watch for the secondary effect.
  - Page the recommendation team.

PREVENTION:
  - ⚠ Every synchronous dependency must have: a timeout, a circuit breaker, and a bulkhead.
    Non-negotiable. This is the "interviewer's favorite probe" because it's universally correct.
  - Async where the result isn't needed in the response (which is most personalization).
  - Alert on dependency latency (p99) independently of error rate. ⚠ Slowness is a leading
    indicator of failure; errors are lagging.
```
**The lesson.** Slow is worse than down (down fails fast; slow consumes resources). Timeouts +
breakers + bulkheads + async are the four mandatory controls on every dependency.

---

## Drill F6 — Split-brain: two leaders

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Scenario.** A scheduled job that sends monthly invoices runs on 50 instances. It uses a Redis
lock with a 30-second TTL. During a GC pause of 45 seconds, one instance loses its lease but
continues running the job. Another instance acquires the lock and also runs it. 500,000 users
are invoiced twice.

**Analysis.**
```
WHY THIS HAPPENED (the classic lease failure):
  1. Instance A acquires the lock (SET key A PX 30000).
  2. A's lock TTL expires because the renewal thread was paused (⚠ GC pause, or a VM freeze,
     or CPU starvation — the pause exceeds the TTL).
  3. B acquires the lock (legitimately — A's lease has expired).
  4. A's pause ends. A still believes it holds the lock (it doesn't know its lease expired) and
     continues executing the job. ⚠ TWO LEADERS.

THE ROOT CAUSE: **Leases cannot be made safe with clocks alone.** Safety depends on:
  (a) clock skew << TTL, (b) the holder never pauses longer than the TTL margin, and (c) the
  resource validates the lease. Any of these failing breaks it. (This is the "lease problem"
  — fundamental, not a bug.)

THE PROPER FIX — DEFENSE IN DEPTH (all four):
  1. **Fencing tokens** (the correct primary fix): the job's side effect (sending invoices)
     must carry a monotonically increasing token (e.g., a sequence number from Redis INCR at
     lock acquisition). The *sink* (the invoice-sending service) rejects any token <= the
     highest it has seen. ⚠ Even if A (with token 100) and B (with token 101) both run, only
     B's invoices are accepted. This converts a correctness bug into an availability hiccup.
  2. **Idempotent side effects**: give each (user, billing_period) a unique idempotency key.
     A second send is a no-op. ⚠ This is the *most reliable* fix because it doesn't depend on
     any lock at all.
  3. Longer TTL + renewal with margin (TTL >> max pause), and ⚠ avoid stop-the-world pauses
     (use a GC that doesn't pause long, or run the job in a process that isn't GC'd — e.g., a
     separate worker, not a web server).
  4. **Verify leadership before the side effect**: immediately before each chunk of work,
     re-check the lock (with the token) and abort if you lost it. ⚠ Not sufficient alone
     (race window), but it narrows the window dramatically.

WHAT YOU'D DO NOW:
  - Stop the double-billing: pause the job, identify duplicates (by idempotency key), issue
    refunds/corrections.
  - Fix: idempotency + fencing + a much longer TTL (e.g., 10 minutes for a job that takes
    5 minutes, renewed periodically).
  - Move the job out of the web servers into a dedicated worker process.

PREVENTION (broader):
  - Never trust a lock alone for a critical side effect. ⚠ Make the effect idempotent.
  - Use fencing tokens for anything a stale leader can touch.
  - Use etcd/Consul lease + session (better) over raw Redis locks, but the idempotency
    requirement remains.
  - ⚠ A safer pattern for this class of job: **partition the work** (e.g., shard the job by
    user_id into N chunks) and run each chunk with its own lease. Smaller blast radius.

INTERVIEW TAKEAWAY: "I make side effects idempotent and use fencing tokens; locks alone are
not a correctness guarantee." This is one of the most senior-sounding sentences in distributed
systems.
```
**The lesson.** Locks/leases are advisory, not authoritative. Idempotency + fencing tokens are
what make correctness independent of the lock.

---

## Drill F7 — Cache stampede

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Scenario.** A celebrity posts. 2 million followers load their feed within 5 minutes. The
feed is cached per user with a 300-second TTL. At t=0 all 2 million caches for that region are
cold.

**Analysis.**
```
WHAT HAPPENS (naive design):
  1. 2M users hit their feed cache. All miss (cold or expired).
  2. All 2M miss → all go to the DB (or the expensive feed-generation path).
  3. ⚠ 2M concurrent DB queries. The DB collapses (it was sized for ~50k QPS).
  4. Even if the DB survives, latency is 10s+ and most requests time out.
  5. ⚠ The viral post ALSO triggers fan-out (12.3.7 hybrid threshold — a celebrity is above
     threshold, so fan-out-on-read, but the read-time fetch for 2M users is itself huge).

THIS IS THE THUMBING HERD / STAMPEDE PROBLEM. It's not just a cache problem; it's a
load-amplification problem (a read-only operation becomes a write-scale load on the DB).

THE FIXES (layer them):
  1. **Single-flight / request coalescing** (⚠ the single most important fix): at the app
     level, if a key is being loaded, other requests for that key wait for the same load
     promise (not launch their own). With 50 app instances this only dedupes per instance;
     ⚠ for global dedupe, use a distributed lock with a short TTL (e.g., 5-10s) around the
     load, or a leader-wins pattern.
  2. **Probabilistic early expiration** (a.k.a. "XFetch"): recompute expiration as
     `exp - delta * random()%K` where delta is a fraction of the TTL. Staggered natural
     expiry instead of synchronized.
  3. **Jittered TTLs**: `TTL = base * (0.9 + 0.2 * random())`. Never let a whole keyspace
     expire simultaneously.
  4. **Serve stale while revalidating** (stale-while-revalidate): serve the cached value
