```
     (even expired) immediately and refresh in the background. ⚠ This is the killer feature
     for read-heavy social content: users see slightly stale content instantly instead of
     waiting.
  5. **CDN-level caching** of feed/item JSON: the edge absorbs the bulk before it reaches
     your app.
  6. **Pre-warm** the celebrity's content into cache ahead of time (on post creation).
  7. **Load shedding** as the safety valve: above a QPS threshold, serve stale/cached only,
     shed DB reads.

⚠ For a truly viral post (astronomical read count), consider **pre-computing the celebrity's
  recent posts once** (a denormalized "hot content" store), so 2M readers hit one cheap key
  rather than 2M individual feed computations.

PREVENTION / DESIGN:
  - Never have a cache TTL that synchronizes across many keys.
  - Warm caches on content creation for high-follower accounts.
  - Use stale-while-revalidate for anything user-facing and read-heavy.
  - Alert on cache miss rate (a spike is the leading indicator of a stampede).
```
**The lesson.** A stampede turns a read into a write-scale load. Coalescing + jittered TTLs +
stale-while-revalidate are the standard trio.

---

## Drill F8 — Retry storm

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Scenario.** A dependency returns 503 for 2% of requests. Your service retries each 503 up to
3 times with exponential backoff (base 100ms). 1000 QPS to the dependency. The dependency's
error rate climbs to 40%.

**Analysis.**
```
THE MATH (why 2% became 40%):
  Base load: 1,000 QPS. 
  2% fail = 20 QPS of failures. Each retries up to 3× → ⚠ up to 60 extra QPS of retries,
  concentrated in a short window (backoff is 100ms, 200ms, 400ms → retries pile up in
  500ms windows). 
  Total ≈ 1,060 QPS but ⚠ the retries are BURSTY (synchronized with the backoff schedule),
  and if the dependency is near its capacity knee, even 6% more load pushes it over →
  error rate explodes non-linearly. 40% is plausible once you're past the knee.

  ⚠ Note: base 2% error with 3 retries should only be 2% × 3 = 6% extra, not 40%. The 40%
  comes from the queueing knee: at the knee, a 6% load increase causes a 38% error increase.
  This is the key lesson: **past the knee, small load increases cause large error increases.**

  Also ⚠: the client-side retry also happens at the *mesh* layer (Envoy retries), so the
  actual multiplier could be 3 (app) × 3 (mesh) = 9×.

THE FIXES:
  1. **Retry budget**: cap retries at ~10% of total requests. ⚠ If the budget is exhausted,
     fail fast (return the error) rather than retrying. This is the single most important
     protection.
  2. **Retry only idempotent operations** and only transient errors (503, 502, timeouts, NOT
     400/404/422).
  3. **Full jitter** (`sleep = random(0, base * 2^attempt)`). ⚠ Without jitter, all clients
     retry at the same time (the "thundering herd" / retry avalanche), which never lets the
     dependency recover.
  4. **One retry layer only**: if the mesh retries, the app doesn't (or vice versa). ⚠ Three
     layers = 3^3 = 27 attempts.
  5. **Circuit breaker**: if the error rate is high, open the breaker (fail fast, no retries).
     Retries inside a breaker; breaker opens only after retries are exhausted.
  6. **Deadline propagation**: retries share a total deadline, not each getting a fresh one.
     This naturally bounds total attempts.

  Why the dependency is at 40% and climbing: past the knee, latency explodes, more requests
  time out, more retries, more load → more timeouts. ⚠ **Metastable failure.** The fix is to
  REDUCE load (open the breaker, shed) to get back below the knee, then recover.

WHAT YOU'D DO NOW:
  - Open the circuit breaker on the dependency (stop sending retries).
  - Shed load at the edge if needed.
  - The dependency recovers once load drops below the knee.

PREVENTION:
  - Configure retry budgets at the mesh/SDK level (Kubernetes services do this by default:
    retries are allowed only up to 20% of non-5xx requests + a minimum of 10/s).
  - Rate-limit retries per client.
  - Circuit breaker with slow-call detection (not just errors).
```
**The lesson.** Past the knee, small load increases cause large error increases (metastable
failure). Retry budgets + jitter + breakers are what keep you on the safe side of the knee.

---

## Drill F9 — Data corruption from a partial write

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Scenario.** A database node crashes mid-transaction. After restart, some rows are missing
and some transactions are partially applied.

**Analysis.**
```
THIS IS A DURABILITY BUG, and the mechanism matters:

DURABILITY (the ACID D) IS ACHIEVED BY THE WRITE-AHEAD LOG (WAL):
  1. Transaction commits. The commit record is written to the WAL and fsynced BEFORE the
     commit is acknowledged to the client.
  2. Data pages may still be in memory (buffer pool); they get flushed later (checkpointing).
  3. On crash: the WAL is replayed (redo) to bring the database to the last committed
     transaction. Uncommitted transactions are rolled back (undo).

  ⚠ THE CRITICAL RULE: **fsync the WAL commit record before acknowledging the commit.** If you
  ack before fsync and the machine loses power, acknowledged writes can be LOST. This is a real
  bug class (some databases had it, some proxies still do).

WHAT CAUSES PARTIAL/CORRUPT DATA:
  - ⚠ **No fsync before ack** (WAL not durable) → lost acknowledged commits.
  - ⚠ Torn writes / partial page writes (hardware) → checksums + WAL prevent this; a torn page
    is detected by checksum and the WAL replays from before it.
  - ⚠ **fsync not durable on the storage layer** (a cloud volume that lies about durability,
    or a consumer SSD with a volatile write cache) → ⚠ data loss on power failure despite
    correct app behavior. This is a real, under-appreciated failure.
  - Bug in the recovery/replay code (rare, but real).

CONSEQUENCES AT SCALE:
  - Partial application of a transaction → ⚠ invariants violated (e.g., order created but
    payment not recorded). This is why ACID exists.
  - Silent corruption is worse than a crash (you don't know what you lost).

THE FIX / PREVENTION:
  1. **Always use a real database with proper WAL + fsync** (don't hand-roll storage).
  2. **Durable storage**: cloud volumes with proper durability guarantees; ⚠ avoid
     network-attached storage with a write-back cache that lies about flush.
  3. **Checksums** on pages and WAL records detect corruption; WAL replay repairs it.
  4. **Point-in-time recovery (PITR)** from WAL + base backups → restore to any point in time.
  5. **Replicas protect against corruption** spreading: if a replica's checksums fail, promote
     a different one. ⚠ But if corruption is in the WAL (rare, catastrophic), PITR to before
     the corruption is the only recovery.
  6. **Backup + verify**: ⚠ a backup you never restore-tested is not a backup.

INTERVIEW ANGLE:
  "This is exactly why I don't roll my own storage, and why PITR + tested restores matter more
  than redundancy. Redundancy protects against hardware failure; durability (fsync) and PITR
  protect against data loss."
```
**The lesson.** Durability = fsync before ack. Redundancy ≠ durability. PITR + tested restores
are the safety net.

---

## Drill F10 — Time skew and lease expiry

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Scenario.** A distributed lock service uses Redis TTL of 30s. Machines A and B have clocks
that differ by 8 seconds (NTP drift during a network partition). A holds the lock; its clock is
5s slow. When A's TTL "expires" (in Redis's clock), A still believes it holds it. B acquires
the lock. Both proceed.

**Analysis.**
```
THE MECHANISM:
  - TTL is computed in Redis's time. Redis thinks the lock expired.
  - A's local clock says "no, I still hold it for 22 more seconds."
  - A continues believing it's the leader. B legitimately acquires.
  - ⚠ TWO LEADERS. This is a split-brain from clock skew.

WHY CLOCKS ARE THE PROBLEM:
  - Crystal oscillators drift ~10–500 ppm → at 500 ppm, ±43s/day. A machine without NTP for a
    day can be tens of seconds off.
  - NTP can fail or be unreachable; VM clocks can be paused during migration/snapshot.
  - ⚠ A 30s TTL with ±8s of skew means A must never pause longer than ~15s AND the clock skew
    must be bounded. Both assumptions are fragile.

THE FIX (defense in depth — this is the important part):
  1. **Fencing tokens** (the primary fix, works regardless of clocks): the lock acquisition
     returns a monotonically increasing token (Redis INCR); every operation carries it; the
     resource rejects tokens <= the highest seen. ⚠ Clocks become irrelevant to safety.
  2. **Bounded clock error**: use a time authority with a known error bound (Google's
     TrueTime, or NTP with monitoring + alerting on skew). Size the TTL >> max skew.
  3. **Longer TTL with margin**: TTL = 5× the maximum expected skew + maximum expected pause.
  4. **Re-validate before irreversible actions**: before any side effect, re-check the lock
     (with token) — narrows the window (⚠ doesn't eliminate it alone).
  5. **Monotonic clocks for local durations**: measure pause duration with a monotonic clock,
     not wall clock (⚠ NTP stepping the wall clock backwards breaks this).

PREVENTION (the general rule):
  - Never use wall-clock-based TTLs for correctness-critical locks without fencing.
  - ⚠ The resource is the last line of defense: make it validate tokens.
  - Keep a distributed clock service (Chrony/NTP) monitored; alert on skew.
  - Prefer etcd/Consul leases over raw Redis TTLs (better primitives), but STILL fence.

INTERVIEW TAKEAWAY:
  "Clocks lie. I use them for TTLs and timestamps, but I never use them as the *only* mechanism
  for correctness. Fencing tokens make the lock's safety independent of time."
```
**The lesson.** Clocks are for humans and TTLs, not for safety. Fencing tokens make correctness
clock-independent.

---

## Drill F11 — Thundering herd on connection recovery

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Scenario.** A brief network outage (30s) affects a region. WebSocket clients all lose their
connections simultaneously. When service returns, 2 million clients reconnect in the same 5
seconds. The auth service and the gateway are CPU-pegged.

**Analysis.**
```
WHAT HAPPENS:
  1. 2M clients reconnect in a 5s burst (they all detect the outage at the same time and
     retry at the same time).
  2. Each reconnect: TLS handshake (expensive, ~1 RTT + crypto), auth token validation
     (a call to the auth service), then the connection is established.
  3. ⚠ Auth service: 2M validation calls in 5s = 400,000 QPS burst. Auth was sized for
     ~20k QPS. → Auth collapses.
  4. Gateway: TLS handshakes are CPU-expensive; 2M in 5s saturates CPU.
  5. Half the clients fail to reconnect → retry → ⚠ more load (retry storm).

THE COMPOUNDING FACTORS:
  - ⚠ TLS handshake cost: no session resumption → full crypto per reconnect.
  - ⚠ Auth is on the critical path for every reconnect.
  - ⚠ Clients retry immediately (no backoff), so failures → retries → saturation.

THE FIXES (design-level, before the incident):
  1. **Jittered reconnect backoff** (client-side): ⚠ exponential backoff with FULL JITTER.
     This is the most important fix — it spreads 2M reconnects over ~30s instead of 5s. ⚠
     Also honor `Retry-After` from the server.
  2. **Connection warming / pre-warming**: keep a pool of pre-established connections (or
     TLS session tickets) so reconnect is cheap. For WebSockets, keep "shadow" connections or
     use a short-lived connection model.
  3. **Auth caching with bounded TTL**: ⚠ cache validated tokens for 5–15 min so reconnect
     doesn't hit auth at all. Trade-off: revocation takes TTL time (acceptable for sessions,
     not for permissions).
  4. **Load-shed / rate-limit reconnects** at the gateway (per-IP and global), with
     `Retry-After`.
  5. **Scale the auth service for the reconnect storm** (autoscale aggressively, or keep
     headroom for a 2× surge event — "N+1" capacity planning for reconnect scenarios).

THE RESILIENCE ANGLE:
  - ⚠ Design for the "everything reconnects at once" scenario. It's a real, predictable
     event (every regional blip, every deploy that drops connections, every failover).
  - Deploys that drop all connections at once (⚠ a "connection drain" that kills all sockets
     instead of draining) create a self-inflicted thundering herd. Use graceful drain +
     client jitter.

INTERVIEW TAKEAWAY:
  "Every mass-reconnect event needs jittered backoff, cached auth, and reconnect rate limits.
  And my deploys must drain connections gracefully, not kill them, or I'm creating the herd
  myself."
```
**The lesson.** Mass reconnects are a predictable thundering herd. Jitter + cached auth +
reconnect rate limits + graceful drains are the answer.

---

## Drill F12 — Partial failure: one bad node in a healthy fleet

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Scenario.** 50 app instances. One instance has a corrupted config (e.g., wrong DB endpoint)
and returns 500 for 5% of requests (it fails 1 in 20 requests). Overall error rate is 0.1%, so
nobody pages. But 2% of users intermittently get errors.

**Analysis.**
```
THE PROBLEM — GRAY FAILURE (a.k.a. partial failure):
  - Overall metrics look healthy (0.1% errors) → ⚠ NO ALERT.
  - But 2% of users are affected 25% of the time → real user pain.
  - The load balancer's health check is a `/healthz` endpoint that returns 200 (the process
    is alive), so ⚠ the bad node stays in the rotation.
  - This is the "one bad node in a healthy fleet" failure — extremely common, extremely
    under-alerted.

WHY IT'S HARD TO DETECT:
  - Aggregate metrics average it away (1/50 nodes = 2% of traffic).
  - Health checks test liveness, not correctness.
  - ⚠ The user experience is affected much more than the dashboard suggests (users on a
    sticky connection or repeatedly hitting the bad node get hit repeatedly).

THE FIXES:
  1. **Outlier detection / passive ejection** (the RIGHT fix): the LB (Envoy, Linkerd,
     cloud ALB) computes per-endpoint success rate and latency, and ejects a statistical
     outlier (⚠ e.g., 5 consecutive errors, or >3σ below the fleet median). The bad node is
     ejected in seconds, no alert needed. ⚠ This is strictly better than active health checks
     for partial failures.
  2. **Per-endpoint alerting**: alert on per-node error rate, not just fleet error rate. ⚠
     "Any single instance with >1% errors for 2 min" is the alert that would have caught this.
  3. **Improved health check**: a readiness probe that checks a real dependency (a lightweight
     DB query). ⚠ Trade-off: this adds DB load (500 pods × 1 check/10s = 50 QPS) and can
     cause false-failures during DB blips (⚠ don't wire this to liveness — see F12 note below).
  4. **Config validation at startup + periodic**: the bad config should have failed a startup
     check ("can I reach the DB?"). Fail fast at boot, not at request time.

⚠ THE LIVENESS/READINESS TRAP:
  If readiness checks a dependency and all 50 pods fail readiness during a 10s DB blip, the
  LB removes ALL pods → total outage (⚠ worse than the original problem). Rule:
  - Liveness = process only (never dependency-dependent). Restart a wedged process.
  - Readiness = "can I serve?" ⚠ MAY check a cached dependency status, but should be
    conservative (don't flap all pods).
  - Better: rely on **outlier detection + per-endpoint alerts** rather than dependency-based
    health checks.

THE BROADER PRINCIPLE:
  - ⚠ Aggregate health metrics hide partial failures. Design monitoring to detect "some users
    are broken" not just "the system is broken": per-tenant, per-endpoint, per-region, and
    **synthetic monitoring from real user geographies**.
  - User-reported errors with trace IDs are how you'd catch this in practice (→ observability).

INTERVIEW TAKEAWAY:
  "Gray failure is the failure mode aggregate metrics miss. I use outlier detection (passive
  ejection), per-endpoint alerting, and synthetic monitoring. And I never wire dependency health
  to liveness."
```
**The lesson.** Partial failure hides in averages. Detect it per-endpoint, not fleet-wide. Never
wire dependency health to liveness.

---

# 12.1-12.4 INTERVIEW Q&A (40 questions with model answers)

> Format: the question as an interviewer would ask it, then a model answer written the way a
> strong candidate would say it out loud. Rehearse the *structure* of the answers, not the exact
> words. Difficulty labels indicate where each question typically lands.

---

### Q1. What's the difference between vertical and horizontal scaling, and when would you choose each?

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Vertical scaling means making one machine bigger — more CPU, RAM, faster disk. Horizontal means
adding more machines behind a router. Vertical is cheaper to implement because there's no
coordination, no data movement, and no new code — it's the right first move, and it stays the
right move as long as your data fits in RAM and one machine can handle the write throughput.
Horizontal is what you need when you exceed one machine's capacity, or when you need redundancy —
and you need redundancy almost immediately, because a single machine, however big, is a single
point of failure no matter how much RAM it has. So in practice: vertical for the database while
it's small, and horizontal for the stateless tier from day one, because adding app servers is
cheaper than a bigger app server."

---

### Q2. Explain load balancing algorithms. Which would you use and why?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"There are about seven worth knowing. Round-robin: next server in rotation — fine for homogeneous
servers with cheap requests, bad when request cost varies. Weighted round-robin: when servers
differ in size. Least-connections: send to the server with the fewest active connections — good
for long-lived connections. Least-outstanding-requests: fewest in-flight requests — this is what
I'd usually use, because it's the most accurate signal of load and it self-corrects. IP or
consistent hashing for sticky sessions — I'd avoid it, it destroys the ability to replace a
failed server. Power-of-two-random-choices for very large fleets of cheap workers, because it
needs no shared state. And least-latency for heterogeneous fleets.

The algorithm is less important than the health check. I'd use least-outstanding-requests behind
an L7 balancer with passive outlier detection, so a partially-broken server gets ejected based
on observed errors rather than a health check. And I'd never add sticky sessions to hide a
statefulness bug — it prevents failover and blocks rolling deploys."

---

### Q3. What is a stateless service and why does it matter?

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"A stateless service keeps no request-specific state in the process. It matters because
statelessness is the precondition for horizontal scaling, for replacing a failed instance
instantly, for rolling deploys without dropping sessions, and for autoscaling — all of which
need the ability to send a request to any instance.

Getting there means inventorying the state: identity state goes into a signed cookie or a session
store; cacheable state goes into a bounded local cache with a short TTL; durable state goes into
the database with an idempotency key; and file state goes into object storage. The discipline is
that any local cache is only acceptable for something a user would call 'slightly wrong' —
never for anything they'd call 'lost money' or 'wrong permission'."

---

### Q4. How do you invalidate a CDN cache?

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"The primary mechanism should be content-addressed URLs — if the content changes, the URL
changes, so there's nothing to invalidate. That works great for static assets like JS and images,
which are immutable by construction, and lets you set a one-year max-age with `immutable`.

For everything else, I'd use short TTLs as the base guarantee and purge only as a targeted
tool. The CDNs that scale do purge by tag — you tag objects with a surrogate key like
`/users/42/avatar` and purge the tag, which is the only purge design that survives fan-out. A
per-URL purge API melts under load.

The most valuable headers are actually `stale-while-revalidate` and `stale-if-error` — they turn
the cache from a performance optimization into an availability mechanism, because users get
instant stale content instead of an error when the origin is slow or down.

The two things that break CDN caching are `Set-Cookie` on responses, which makes them
uncacheable by default, and `Vary` headers — every value of a varied header multiplies your
object count and fragments your hit rate."

---

### Q5. When would you add read replicas, and what's the risk?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"When reads dominate writes — typically a read:write ratio of 100:1 or more — replicas are almost
always the right next step, because they multiply read capacity roughly linearly and cost nothing
in coordination.

The risk is read-your-writes. If a user updates their profile and the next GET hits a replica
with a second of lag, they see the old value, and users report that as a bug even though
'eventually consistent' is technically true. The mitigations are: route that user's reads to the
primary for a short window after a write, use a per-user sticky route for a bounded time, or
pass a minimum version and have the replica wait until it has applied that version.

The second risk is failover. If the primary dies and you promote an asynchronous replica, you
lose every acknowledged-but-not-replicated write. So replicas are a read-scaling tool, and if
you need RPO=0 you need synchronous replication — which is a different design with a write
latency cost."

---

### Q6. What is the connection pool problem with Postgres and how do you solve it?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Postgres creates an OS process per connection, roughly 5–10 MB each, and its default
`max_connections` is 100. With 200 app instances times 20 connections, you have 4,000 backend
processes. That's tens of gigabytes of memory spent on processes that are mostly idle, plus
scheduler and context-switch thrash. Postgres also performs badly when connection count exceeds
roughly two to four times core count — the lock manager and scheduler become the bottleneck
before CPU does.

The fix is a connection proxy: PgBouncer in transaction mode multiplexes thousands of client
connections onto a few dozen server connections. Transaction mode means it returns the server
connection after each transaction, which requires that your app not rely on session state — no
LISTEN/NOTIFY across transactions, no temp tables, no advisory locks held across a transaction,
no SET that isn't reset. If you need query-aware routing, caching, or read/write splitting,
ProxySQL does that.

The mental model I use: don't think of 'as many connections as possible' — think of 'a small
number of connections per instance, behind a proxy that multiplexes.'"

---

### Q7. Explain partitioning vs sharding.

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Partitioning splits one table inside one database into many physical tables. It prunes I/O and
enables instant retention — dropping a monthly partition instead of deleting two billion rows — but
it doesn't increase write throughput beyond one instance, and it can hurt, because more tables
means more planning overhead and more open files.

Sharding splits data across multiple database instances. Now you're horizontal, and you've
crossed a one-way door: no joins across shards, no cross-shard transactions, rebalancing is a
multi-day operation, and unique constraints become unique-within-a-shard.

The decision rule is access pattern and lifecycle. If queries are time-bounded or you need
retention, partition by time. If one machine can't hold the data or can't take the writes, shard
by hash or by tenant. And you should pick the shard key by the dominant query, not by even
distribution — shard by whatever the queries read by, so those queries stay local."

---

### Q8. Why is modulo-based sharding a problem, and what is consistent hashing?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Modulo sharding has the property that changing N reshuffles almost everything. Going from 8
shards to 9 means only about one in nine keys land on the same shard, so you're migrating
essentially your entire dataset — offline — for a resize. That makes the shard count effectively
fixed forever, which is unacceptable.

Consistent hashing puts nodes and keys on a ring by hash, so adding or removing a node only moves
roughly 1/N of the keys. But vanilla consistent hashing has bad load balance, because each node
owns one big random arc. The fix is virtual nodes: give each physical node 100 to 500 points on
the ring, so its share concentrates tightly around 1/N.

There's a subtlety worth knowing: real systems like Dynamo use ranged hashing — contiguous ranges
of the ring assigned to nodes — because that gives you range scans, natural replicas per range,
and the ability to split a single hot range. And for small N, rendezvous or highest-random-weight
hashing is simpler and provably moves the minimal number of keys with no ring to maintain."

---

### Q9. What are virtual nodes and why do you need them?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"In consistent hashing, each node occupies one arc of the ring, and those arcs are random
variables. With three nodes it's quite likely one node gets 70% of the ring. That's a real
load-imbalance problem, and you can't fix it after the fact.

Virtual nodes solve it: each physical node registers 100 to 500 points on the ring, so it owns
100 to 500 small arcs. The law of large numbers concentrates each node's total share tightly
around 1/N, so the variance collapses. With 5 nodes and 256 vnodes each, the maximum deviation
from perfect balance is a few percent.

The practical requirements: the vnode set must be computable identically by every node, so the
hash function, node identity, and vnode index all have to be agreed on; and you should make the
vnodes-per-node a parameter, so you can tune the ratio without re-adding all nodes."

---

### Q10. What is backpressure and load shedding?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Backpressure is a mechanism for a slow consumer to slow down a fast producer, so that a
throughput problem doesn't become a latency problem and then an out-of-memory problem. The
simplest and most important form is a bounded queue — every queue must have a max size, and on
overflow you either block the producer or reject. Beyond that: caller-runs semantics, reactive
streams' demand-driven flow control, credit-based flow control like Kafka and gRPC, and adaptive
concurrency limits.

Load shedding is deliberately dropping or deferring work so a subset of users gets a fast failure
instead of everyone getting a slow one. The patterns in increasing order of politeness: cap
in-flight requests at the edge; shed low-priority work first — analytics, prefetch, background —
to protect the user-facing path; propagate deadlines so a downstream hop doesn't work on a
request nobody is waiting for; and have a documented degradation ladder that says what to drop
first.

The reason this matters is queueing theory: past the knee, adding work makes things worse for
everyone, and overload collapse is worse than overload. Retries make it far worse, which is why
clients need backoff with full jitter and a retry budget."

---

### Q11. What's the difference between CPU-based and queue-depth-based autoscaling?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"CPU is a lagging proxy. For an HTTP service handling variable-cost requests, CPU reflects
utilization, not demand — and by the time CPU is high, latency is already climbing. But CPU is
stable and cheap to measure, which is why it's the default.

Queue depth is the truth for asynchronous workers. If you have a backlog of 1 million messages,
adding CPU only helps if the bottleneck is CPU; if it's an external API rate limit, more
consumers just make the throttling worse. So scale queue consumers on backlog count or, better,
on the age of the oldest message — because a shrinking backlog of slow-to-process messages is
still a latency violation.

For latency-sensitive APIs, neither is great: concurrency or in-flight requests is the signal
that predicts latency. And for known patterns — a 9am traffic spike, a game launch — scheduled
or predictive scaling gets capacity in place before the spike, with reactive scaling as the
backstop. The biggest real-world gotcha in all of this is cold start: image pull plus JIT warmup
plus cache fill means a new instance is useless for 30 to 90 seconds, so reactive scaling
inherently loses the first minutes of every spike."

---

### Q12. Active-active vs active-passive?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Active-passive is simpler: one region writes, the other is a hot standby. You get clean
consistency because there's a single writer, and failover is a routing switch. The costs are that
you pay for idle capacity, and failover takes as long as promotion plus DNS propagation — think
minutes — and you're regionally vulnerable the whole time.

Active-active gives every region local latency and no failover event at all, because no region
failing is a failure. The cost is that write conflict resolution becomes your problem: you
either route writes to a home region per entity, or you accept last-write-wins and lose
concurrent writes, or you go full multi-master with vector clocks and reconciliation.

The decision is fundamentally latency versus consistency. Strict consistency across continents
means every write pays 70 to 150 milliseconds of extra RTT. So my default is: single region or
active-passive until the latency SLO forces otherwise, then active-active with a home region per
entity and a defined consistency budget per operation."

---

### Q13. Explain the CAP theorem properly.

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"The precise statement is: in the presence of a network partition, a distributed system must
choose between consistency and availability. Two things it's often misquoted as: it doesn't say
you can't have both C and A — you can, whenever there's no partition — and it doesn't say you
choose at design time; the choice only exists during a partition, which is usually rare.

The more useful refinement for engineers is PACELC: if there's a Partition, choose A or C; Else,
choose Latency or Consistency. That matters because the latency-versus-consistency trade exists on
every single normal request, while the CAP trade only exists during the rare partition. So the
practical design is: in steady state, choose low latency with bounded staleness; during a
partition, choose per operation — CP for money and permissions, AP for likes and presence — and
have an explicit reconciliation story for the AP parts."

---

### Q14. Is your design CP or AP?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"That question doesn't have an answer for a system as a whole, so let me be specific per
operation. Payments, idempotency-key registration, permissions, and seat reservation are CP — I
reject on the minority side rather than risk a double-spend or a permission violation. Likes,
view counts, presence, and recommendations are AP — every replica accepts, and I reconcile with
read-repair and counters. Reads are local with a stated staleness bound, which is a PACELC 'E'
choice, not a CAP choice.

The thing I want to be explicit about is that 'AP' is not a free pass. If two replicas accept
writes concurrently and you have no conflict-resolution strategy, AP is just data loss. So every
AP write path in my design names what happens on conflict — usually a hybrid logical clock with
last-write-wins for scalars, or read-repair plus a sibling for anything a user would notice."

---

### Q15. Explain consensus (Raft) at a level you can defend.

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Consensus lets a set of nodes agree on a totally ordered log despite crashes, message loss, and
reordering, with no shared memory and no reliable clock. It's only useful for small, critical
coordination state — service discovery, config, locks, membership, sequence allocation — because
consensus plus state-machine replication means every write pays a round trip before it's
acknowledged.

The 2f+1 rule: five nodes tolerate two failures. The mechanics are a leader that orders
operations and replicates them in parallel, committing once a majority has stored an entry, then
applying to a deterministic state machine. Followers that stop hearing from the leader start a
randomized election timeout and become candidates; winning a majority makes you leader.

The safety argument is three rules. Election restriction: a candidate can only win if its log is
at least as up to date as a majority's, which implies a leader's log contains every committed
entry. Log matching: if two logs share an index and term, they're identical up to that point.
And leader completeness follows from those two. Liveness needs randomized timeouts, because the
FLP result says deterministic consensus can't guarantee termination in an async system with even
one crash.

Read paths need care: the correct approach is ReadIndex — confirm leadership by exchanging
heartbeats, then read locally without a log round trip. Lease-based reads are faster but depend
on clock bounds, and clocks lie."

---

### Q16. What is a split brain and how do you prevent it?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Split-brain is two nodes each acting as authoritative leader. The causes are: a network
partition where each side believes it has a majority; a lease that expires because the holder was
GC-paused or frozen, and which resumes still believing it holds the lease; clock skew making a
TTL look valid on two nodes; and stale routing sending traffic to both.

Prevention is layered. First, quorum intersection — never make a safety decision on a minority,
so 2f+1 nodes and quorum-based election. Second, fencing tokens: the leader gets a
monotonically increasing token from the coordination store, and every write to the protected
resource carries it and is rejected if it's lower than the highest seen. That's the important
one, because it converts a correctness problem into an availability problem — a stale leader
does no damage regardless of what it believes. Third, longer TTLs with margin, and don't suspend
the leader process. Fourth, make the side effect itself idempotent, so a double-run is harmless."

---

### Q17. What is an idempotency key and when do you need it?

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Any time a retry could duplicate a side effect, you need one. The pattern: the client generates
a key, the server stores the key, the request hash, and the response, and does the insert in the
same transaction as the business write. A retry finds the key and replays the stored response
verbatim, so the user gets the same answer, not just the same effect.

The details that matter: scope the key per user and endpoint, store a hash of the request body so
a mismatched reuse is a 422, keep keys for longer than your retry window, and — this is the bug
everyone makes — the key record has to be committed in the same transaction as the work, or you
can record a key and then fail the work, and every retry says 'already done' for something that
never happened.

You need it for payments, order placement, reservations, signup, webhook receivers, and message
consumers. And where you have a database and a message broker, the transactional outbox is the
companion pattern: write the change and an outbox row in one transaction, then relay to the
broker, and let idempotent consumers handle at-least-once delivery."

---

### Q18. What is the Transactional Outbox pattern and why?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"You need to write to your database and publish to a message broker atomically, and you can't —
they're separate systems with no shared transaction. The naive options both lose data: write DB
then publish risks losing the message if you crash in between; publish then write DB risks a
duplicate message and a phantom event.

The outbox pattern: in one database transaction, update the aggregate and insert a row into an
outbox table. A separate relay polls the outbox — `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` so multiple
relays can run — publishes to Kafka, and marks rows as published. Delivery is at-least-once, and
consumers are idempotent, so effectively-once.

The cost is polling latency and an extra table that needs cleanup. The lower-latency alternative
is CDC: read the database's WAL directly with something like Debezium, so there's no polling
interval. Either way, consumer idempotency is mandatory, because at-least-once is the only
guarantee you'll get."

---

### Q19. What is a saga, and choreography vs orchestration?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"A saga decomposes a distributed transaction into local transactions, each with a compensating
action, coordinated by messages or by a central orchestrator. There's no blocking and no 2PC, so
you get availability instead of atomicity, and you pay in eventual consistency and visible
intermediate states.

Choreography is event-driven: each service subscribes and reacts, the flow is implicit in code.
It decouples beautifully and there's no bottleneck, but the flow becomes invisible — nobody can
answer 'what happens if step four fails' without reading five codebases — and as participants
grow it becomes event spaghetti.

Orchestration has a central coordinator that holds a durable state machine and issues commands. The
flow is explicit and versioned, timeouts and SLAs are centralized, long-running and human-in-the-
loop steps are natural, and it's far easier to test.

My rule: orchestration when it's a procedure with business meaning — checkout, provisioning,
fulfillment, anything involving money. Choreography when it's a set of independent reactions —
notify, index, cache, analytics.

And two things that matter more than the choice: compensation is not rollback, it's a new forward
action, like a refund as a new credit entry in an append-only ledger; and you should do the
irreversible step last, so you charge after you reserve, not before."

---

### Q20. Why not just use 2PC for distributed transactions?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Because 2PC is blocking. Participants that have voted prepare must wait for the coordinator's
decision. If the coordinator crashes, or if the network partitions between the coordinator and a
participant, those participants hold their locks and block — potentially for the lifetime of the
transaction. A single coordinator failure becomes a database-wide lock storm. On top of that, a
commit costs two to three round trips inside the critical path, and the coordinator is both a
bottleneck and a single point of failure.

The practical answer is that you don't need cross-system atomicity as often as you think you do.
Spend the transactional guarantee on one store you control — Postgres, or a Spanner — and treat
cross-service state as eventually consistent via the outbox pattern plus a saga with
compensation. Where 2PC is acceptable is inside a cluster you own, on a small homogeneous group of
replicas, which is exactly what etcd and ZooKeeper do internally: consensus over one replicated log,
never two-phase commit across heterogeneous external systems."

---

### Q21. Explain read-your-writes and how you implement it.

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Read-your-writes means a client never sees a state older than its own write. It's the
consistency guarantee users actually notice, and it's the one that breaks when you introduce
replicas.

There are three implementations, in increasing order of cost. One: route that user's reads to the
primary for a short window after a write — simple, but couples reads to the primary and hurts
read scalability for heavy writers. Two: per-user sticky routing for a bounded window. Three, the
best general one: put a version or ETag in the write response, let the client send `min_version`
on subsequent reads, and have the replica wait — with a short timeout — until it has applied that
version, then fall back to the primary if it can't catch up.

In the API I'd make this explicit rather than hidden: return the version in every write response,
accept it as a read parameter, and let callers choose. That way freshness is a contract rather
than an accident."

---

### Q22. Why can't we have consistent, available, and low-latency systems?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Because of the speed of light and because consistency requires agreement. If two clients write
concurrently and both must see the writes in the same order, then before the second write can be
acknowledged, the system must know the first write's outcome — which means either the second
client waits for a round trip to the first, or the system uses a leader and the write is
serialized through it.

Concretely: a linearizable write acknowledged to a client in region A must be visible to a
subsequent read in region B. That's a round trip to region B, or the write must be synchronously
replicated there — which is the same round trip, about 70 to 150 milliseconds across an ocean.
You can have consistency and availability, but only by paying latency. You can have low latency
and availability, but only by accepting staleness. Consistency and low latency together require
you to be in one place — which is why single-region systems can be both fast and consistent, and
why global systems pick which one they give up."

---

### Q23. What are the consistency models, and which would you use where?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"From strongest: linearizable — operations appear to take effect instantaneously, which you get
with consensus; sequential — all nodes see the same order but not necessarily real-time order;
causal — causally related operations are seen in order everywhere, which needs vector clocks;
then read-your-writes, monotonic reads, and monotonic writes, which are session-level guarantees;
then eventual consistency, which converges; and bounded staleness, which is eventual with a
time bound.

The practical advice is: set the consistency requirement per operation, per field, not per
system. Payments, inventory commit, permissions, and uniqueness checks are linearizable. User
profiles are bounded-stale at about a second, or read-your-writes. Feeds, likes, and search are
eventual with a stated bound. Analytics is batch.

And remember that read consistency is a separate choice from write consistency — that's the trap
when you add replicas. N writes to the primary and reads from replicas means your read
consistency is exactly your replication lag, whether you designed for it or not."

---

### Q24. Explain timeouts, retries, circuit breakers, and hedging.

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"These are the four controls on failure, and they work at different layers.

Timeouts bound the wait. Every call needs one. And critically, propagate deadlines rather than
timeouts — pass an absolute deadline downstream so a hop with 40 milliseconds left doesn't start
a 100-millisecond query. Set timeouts around p99.9 of the dependency, always less than the
caller's own timeout.

Retries handle transient failure, with three rules: only retry idempotent operations, use
exponential backoff with full jitter — the jitter is what breaks synchronization — and cap
retries with a budget, around 10 percent of requests, so the system always has capacity for
first-time traffic. Also, pick one retry layer; app plus SDK plus service mesh is three to the
third, which is 27 attempts.

Circuit breakers fail fast when a dependency is unhealthy. Their real value isn't saving the
error, it's freeing up threads and connections so the rest of your service can keep working.
Scope them per dependency and per call site.

Hedging fixes tail latency: after p95, send a second request to a different replica and take the
first success. On a heavy-tailed latency distribution the second request has an independent
chance of being fast, so p99 improves a lot for about 5 to 10 percent extra load. Only hedge
below a load threshold, though — doubling load during an incident is exactly when you can't
afford it."

---

### Q25. How do you choose a timeout?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Three inputs. First, the dependency's own latency distribution under normal load — I want
around p99.9, not the max, because a max-based timeout either fires on legitimate tail events or
never fires. Second, my own remaining deadline: the timeout must be shorter than the caller's
budget, leaving room for me to return my own error, which is much better for the user than a
generic gateway timeout. Third, the cost of waiting versus the cost of failing — if the call is
optional, like recommendations, a short timeout and skip is right; if it's required, a longer
timeout is right.

The trap is a single global timeout. Different operations need different values: a metadata lookup
at 100 milliseconds, a search at one second, a batch export at a minute. And the other trap is
unbounded waits — no timeout at all turns one slow dependency into thread-pool exhaustion and a
whole-service outage."

---

### Q26. What is a bulkhead and why do you need one?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"A bulkhead is a resource pool per dependency or feature, so one starving resource can't consume
everything. It's named after ship compartments for a reason.

Concretely: a separate thread pool and connection pool per downstream service, so the search
service's 4-second latency can't exhaust the threads that checkout needs. Shuffle-sharding across
per-tenant pools so a noisy tenant can't starve others. And the strongest bulkhead is a separate
deployment — if recommendations are in their own service and their own pods, their failure can't
touch checkout at all, which no in-process pool achieves.

In one outage I worked through, a search dependency went slow, exhausted a shared 200-thread
pool, and took down checkout, profile, and everything else — one slow dependency, whole-service
outage. Bulkheads are the structural fix: the pool for search had 10 threads, search degraded to
returning nothing, and checkout never noticed."

---

### Q27. How do you rate limit at scale?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Token bucket is the algorithm I'd default to — tokens refill at a rate, the bucket holds a
burst allowance, and each request takes one. It allows controlled bursts while enforcing the
long-term average, which is what APIs need.

The important design decision is that you limit cost, not requests. A search costs ten times a
read, so a request-count limit doesn't control resource consumption. I use hierarchical limits —
per-user, per-tenant, and global — and reject if any is exceeded.

For distribution, the two-layer approach: a local token bucket in each instance for the hot path,
with no network hop, plus a global bucket in Redis for fairness across instances, enforced with
an atomic Lua script so check-and-decrement can't race. Also do per-IP limiting at the CDN or
edge, which absorbs volumetric abuse for free.

Two operational notes: always return 429 with Retry-After, or clients hammer. And the limiter
itself must be protected — if Redis fails, decide per endpoint whether you fail open for
availability or fail closed for security, and have a local fallback."

---

### Q28. What are CRDTs and when would you use one?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"A CRDT is a data type whose replicas can be updated without coordination and still converge to
the same value. The theory is that convergence comes from three properties — commutativity,
associativity, and idempotency — and if your merge operation has all three, replicas converge
regardless of order or duplication.

The canonical use is collaborative text, where last-write-wins would lose one person's edits.
Each character insertion gets a unique causally-ordered ID, and all replicas merge the set of
insertions by that order. That's the basis of CRDT-based editors and offline-first apps.

Where I'd be careful: CRDTs converge, but they don't necessarily converge to something correct
for the business. They can't enforce arbitrary invariants like balance ≥ 0 or capacity ≤ 100. And
a scalar that two users set to different values isn't a technical conflict — it's a product
conflict that sometimes needs a human. So: CRDT for user-visible collaborative conflicts, bounded
staleness for ordinary reads, strong consistency for money. And CRDT metadata needs
causality-tracked garbage collection, which is a distributed-systems problem in itself."

---

### Q29. How do you handle ordering without a global clock?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"You first decide what order you actually need. Most systems don't need a global total order —
they need per-entity order, like per conversation or per feed. So assign a per-entity sequence
number at ingest, from a database sequence, a Redis increment, or the Kafka partition offset, and
let clients dedupe and reorder. That's cheap and correct for the requirement.

When you do need cross-node causality, Lamport clocks give you a consistent order that respects
happens-before, but they can't detect concurrency — equal counters mean concurrent. Vector clocks
detect concurrency precisely but cost metadata proportional to node count.

For real systems the best modern answer is hybrid logical clocks, which produce timestamps that
are both causally ordered and close to physical time, so TTLs and human-readable timestamps still
work. And for correctness decisions, never trust a wall clock across machines — use HLC, a
database version, or a consensus log index. The rule I hold to: wall clocks are for humans and
for TTLs with slack; they're never the sole basis for a correctness decision."

---

### Q30. What is a lease and what's the lease problem?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"A lease says 'you may act as leader for a fixed duration, and the grantor expires it after
that.' The problem is that you cannot make a lease safe with clocks alone. For safety you need
bounded clock skew, you need the holder never to pause longer than the TTL margin, and you need
the resource to validate the lease. Any one of those failing means two nodes can both believe
they hold it.

The mitigations are: conservative TTLs sized as a multiple of maximum clock skew plus maximum
expected pause; avoiding process suspension for the leader; and — the real fix — fencing tokens.
The coordination store hands out a monotonically increasing number with the lease, every operation
carries it, and the protected resource rejects anything lower than the highest it's seen. That
makes safety independent of clocks, because a stale leader simply can't do damage no matter what
it believes."

---

### Q31. How do you detect that a node is down?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"You can't prove it's down, so you estimate. There are three approaches.

Active heartbeats are simple and fast, but they have two failure modes: a frozen process keeps
answering heartbeats, and a network blip makes a live node look dead — which is what causes
split-brain.

Passive detection is more robust in practice: clients observe errors and latency to a peer and
eject it after N failures or a failure rate over a window. It needs no cooperation, and it only
detects failures you actually hit.

Suspicion is the best of both, and it's what Cassandra and Dynamo use. Track heartbeat
inter-arrival as an EWMA; when it exceeds a threshold, mark the node suspected, not dead; gossip
that suspicion; and only confirm when others agree. Cassandra formalizes this as the PHI
accrual failure detector, which converts the inter-arrival distribution into a probability and
adapts the threshold to current network conditions. The distinction between suspected and dead is
what prevents a transient congestion event from cascading into a mass ejection."

---

### Q32. What is quorum and what's the R + W > N rule?

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"With N replicas, a write goes to W and a read returns from R. If R + W > N, the read quorum and
the write quorum must overlap, so the read is guaranteed to see the write. With N=3, W=2, R=2
works; W=1, R=1 doesn't, and can return stale data.

Two caveats worth stating, because the rule is often quoted without them. First, in real systems
reads are often 'sloppy quorum' — the coordinator returns a value from any live replica, possibly
stale, to satisfy the read — so R+W>N alone doesn't guarantee much when you can partition. Second,
the practical cost is that a quorum write is as slow as its slowest replica, and a quorum read is
as slow as the R-th fastest, not the fastest. So W=3 against a slow replica makes every write as
slow as that replica.

In practice most systems aren't quorum systems at all — they're single-leader replication, which
is simpler and gives ordering, and they use quorum reasoning for the leaderless Dynamo-style
stores. Quorums are the right tool for counters, where losing an increment is unacceptable."

---

### Q33. Your cache is down. What happens and what do you do?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"It becomes a load multiplier, not a load-neutral event. If you had a 97 percent hit rate at 15,000
QPS, you were sending 450 QPS to the database. Now you're sending all 15,000 — and because a
cache-aside miss on a 50-item page becomes 50 item reads, you might be looking at 750,000
reads a second against a database sized for tens of thousands.

The response, in order: open the circuit breaker on Redis so retries stop; deliberately shed —
accept maybe 2,000 QPS and reject the rest with 503 and Retry-After; serve from any local
in-process cache you have; disable non-essential features to reduce query load; and cap database
connections so the origin can't be taken down too.

For recovery, don't warm the cache at full rate — you'll stampede again. Warm gradually, add
jittered TTLs, and enable single-flight.

And structurally: the reason this hurts so much is that your hit rate is doing the load-bearing
work and the miss path is untested. Test it. Also, a local cache in front of Redis is worth a
lot here, because it survives a Redis outage."

---

### Q34. What's the difference between throughput and latency, and why do they trade off?

🟢 Beginner · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Throughput is work per second; latency is time per unit of work. You can raise one without the
other: batching raises both, caching raises throughput a lot and latency somewhat, and adding
servers raises throughput but leaves per-request latency roughly flat until you cross the knee.

They trade off through utilization. As utilization approaches one, response time goes up
non-linearly — for an M/M/1 queue, latency is roughly service time divided by one minus
utilization, so at 99 percent utilization you're waiting about a hundred times the service time.
That's why you add capacity before the knee, not after, and why a system past the knee can get
*worse* when you add load.

The practical consequence is Little's Law: concurrency equals throughput times latency. At 2,000
QPS and 100 milliseconds you're holding about 200 concurrent requests. That's how you turn a
latency problem into a connection-pool problem, which is how you turn a latency problem into an
outage."

---

### Q35. Design a rate limiter that survives Redis being down.

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Two layers. A local token bucket in every instance, so there's always something enforcing a
limit with no network hop; and a global bucket in Redis for fairness across instances. Requests
must pass both, and the local bucket is what actually sheds load spikes while Redis is degraded.

For Redis failure, fail open with a local fallback rate rather than failing closed, unless the
endpoint is security-sensitive, in which case fail closed. And return 429 with Retry-After plus
the standard RateLimit-* headers, because clients that don't know when to back off just hammer.

Two details people miss: the check-and-decrement must be atomic, so a Lua script or a transaction
— otherwise you over-admit under concurrency. And rate-limit cost, not request count, or a
tenant running one very expensive query a second still destroys you."

---

### Q36. Explain read replicas, connection proxies, caching, sharding, and partitioning — when would you use each?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"In increasing order of cost. Caching hides reads — cache-aside with a TTL, plus single-flight to
stop stampedes, and it's the right first move for hot, recomputable data. Read replicas multiply
read capacity roughly linearly, and the risk is read-your-writes, which you handle with routing or
version fences. Connection proxies solve a problem people forget: Postgres connection counts, not
query capacity, is often the limit, and PgBouncer in transaction mode multiplexes thousands of
clients onto dozens of backends.

Partitioning splits one table to prune I/O and make retention instant via dropping a monthly
partition — but it doesn't buy write throughput. Sharding splits across instances and does buy
write throughput and capacity, at the cost of no joins, no cross-shard transactions, and painful
rebalancing.

The order I actually follow is: fix the query, cache, replicas, vertical scale, denormalize, a
connection proxy, then partition, then shard. Most 'scale to a billion users' answers skip
straight to sharding, and they're wrong — a billion users often just needed an index."

---

### Q37. What is the two-request model for CDNs and why does it matter?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"A cache miss costs two RTTs and exposes your origin: the edge forwards to origin, waits, caches,
and serves. One un-cacheable hot object therefore becomes a self-inflicted DDoS, because every
request pays full origin latency and origin bandwidth.

Mitigations, in order: make things cacheable in the first place — the single biggest lever is
getting Cache-Control right. Use a shield or mid-tier so N simultaneous misses for one object
become one origin fetch. Use cache collaboration so POPs share fills. And use signed URLs or
signed cookies so you can cache personalized content without caching per-user variants.

The operational consequence is that a cache purge is a miss storm. So purge by tag, not by URL;
prefer soft purge — mark stale and revalidate — over hard purge; and prefer versioned URLs so
invalidation isn't needed at all."

---

### Q38. How do you handle a dependency that's slow but not down?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Slow is worse than down, because down fails fast and slow consumes resources. A dependency at 4
seconds with 15,000 QPS means 60,000 concurrent in-flight requests, which exhausts your thread
pool in milliseconds and takes down every endpoint sharing it — that's the cascading failure.

The fixes: a short timeout plus deadline propagation so the hop fails fast rather than working on
a request nobody is waiting for; a circuit breaker with a slow-call-rate threshold, not just an
error threshold; a bulkhead so the slowness is contained; and, best, make the dependency async or
precomputed — if recommendations don't need to be real-time, don't call them in the request.

And I'd raise a generic point: alert on dependency latency independently of errors, because
slowness is a leading indicator and errors are a lagging one. By the time you're seeing errors,
the queue is already full and the recovery is much slower."

---

### Q39. What's your approach to a schema migration on a large table?

🟡 Intermediate · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Expand-contract, always. First release adds the new column as nullable, or adds the new table,
while the old code keeps working. Second, backfill — but in batches, with a rate limit, because a
single 500-million-row update generates enormous WAL, blocks, and can take the table offline.
Third, dual-write: new writes go to both old and new. Fourth, verify with a comparison job. Fifth,
switch reads to the new. Sixth, remove the old path in a later release, after usage telemetry
confirms nothing reads it.

The discipline is that every intermediate state must be correct and independently shippable,
because you never get to do all six steps at once — a deploy, an incident, or a schema revert
will interleave. And on a sharded table, you also have to roll the migration across every shard,
which means a migration orchestrator that tracks per-shard state."

---

### Q40. What would you do differently at 100× the current scale?

🔴 Advanced · Must-know

**Model answer.**
"Separating two kinds of problems: capacity problems, which are solvable with money, and decision
problems, which are not.

Capacity: the storage and bandwidth numbers grow 100× and are handled by object storage and CDN —
that part never needs redesign. Read QPS grows 100× and is handled by the stateless tier and the
cache. Those are budgeting problems.

Decisions: the shard key, the consistency model per operation, and the cache key design are the
three things that are expensive or impossible to change later. So those are what I need to get
right now, at 1× scale. A wrong shard key means an online migration of everything; a wrong
consistency choice means a product redesign.

And then there's the organizational stage: at 100×, the operational surface — hundreds of
instances, deploys, on-call — becomes the dominant constraint, and that's where cells,
progressive delivery, and strong on-call processes start paying for themselves. The design gets
*simpler*, not more complex, as scale increases, because cell isolation lets you reuse a simple
design 64 times."

---

# 12.1-12.4 RAPID-FIRE (60)

> One-line answers. Cover the answer, say it out loud, keep moving. Target: 8–15 seconds each.
> If you miss one, note it and revisit; these are the terms that leak out mid-answer and cost you
> credibility when you pause searching for vocabulary.

1. **Latency of a main-memory access?** 🟢 ~100 ns. (DRAM is ~100× slower than L3 — the cliff.)
2. **Latency of an SSD random read?** 🟢 ~50–100 μs (NVMe; spinning disk is 1–10 ms).
3. **Latency of a same-DC RPC?** 🟢 ~0.1–1 ms including serialization — roughly 1000× a local call.
4. **Cross-continent RTT?** 🟢 ~70–150 ms. Physics: light in fiber travels at ~2/3 c.
5. **Postgres indexed point query?** 🟢 ~1–5 ms (~10 ms if it touches disk).
6. **Redis GET, same region?** 🟢 ~0.5–1 ms.
7. **What is Little's Law?** 🟢 Concurrency = throughput × latency. Turns latency into connection counts.
8. **Why does p99 matter more than p50?** 🔴 At 10k QPS, 1% is 100 req/s — errors become a product problem.
9. **Why is latency nonlinear near 100% utilization?** 🔴 Queueing: M/M/1 latency ≈ service time / (1 − ρ).
10. **What is "overload collapse"?** 🔴 Past the knee, added load *reduces* throughput; more servers can hurt.
11. **Default load-balancing algorithm you'd use?** 🟡 Least outstanding requests, behind L7 with outlier detection.
12. **L4 vs L7 load balancer?** 🟡 L4 for throughput and non-HTTP/long-lived; L7 for path routing, TLS, caching, WAF.
13. **What is slow start on a load balancer?** 🟡 Ramp traffic to newly healthy targets so cold instances aren't overloaded.
14. **Liveness vs readiness probe?** 🟢 Liveness = "restart me?" (dependency-free). Readiness = "route to me?"
15. **Why must liveness not check the database?** 🔴 A DB blip restarts the whole fleet → connection storm → worse outage.
16. **What is outlier detection?** 🟡 LB evicts a per-endpoint statistical outlier on observed errors/latency.
17. **Sharding modulus problem?** 🟡 `hash % N`: going N→N+1 remaps nearly all keys → full offline migration.
18. **Consistent hashing move cost?** 🟢 ~1/N of keys on add or remove.
19. **Why virtual nodes?** 🟡 Vanilla consistent hashing gives uneven arcs; vnodes concentrate each node's share near 1/N.
20. **Rendezvous / HRW hashing vs consistent hashing?** 🔴 No ring to maintain; provably minimal key movement; O(N) hashes per lookup.
21. **What is a hinted handoff?** 🟡 A neighbour accepts writes for an unavailable node and later forwards them.
22. **Read repair?** 🟡 On read, the coordinator overwrites stale replicas with the newest value.
23. **Anti-entropy / Merkle trees?** 🔴 Periodic full comparison of replicas to repair divergence read repair misses (cold data).
24. **Sharding key for a feed app?** 🟡 `user_id` — co-locates a user's data, matches the dominant query.
25. **What does a hot key look like?** 🔴 One celebrity with 10M followers → one shard/key at 100k QPS.
26. **Mitigate a hot key?** 🟡 Split the keyspace for that tenant, cache at the edge, per-key replica fan-out, sample.
27. **Range vs hash partitioning?** 🟢 Range = time-bounded queries + instant retention; hash = even writes, no range scans.
28. **Why partition by time?** 🟢 `DROP PARTITION` for retention is instant; `DELETE` of billions of rows is not.
29. **Read replica lag typical?** 🟡 0–2 s idle; seconds under load; can be minutes during failover.
30. **Read-your-writes implementation?** 🟡 Primary routing for a window, per-user sticky route, or `min_version` + replica wait.
31. **What does `stale-while-revalidate` do?** 🟡 Serve stale immediately, refresh in background. Turns cache into availability.
32. **What does `stale-if-error` do?** 🟡 Serve stale if the origin errors — pure availability insurance.
33. **Cache-aside vs write-through?** 🟢 Cache-aside (lazy, app reads on miss) is the default; write-behind risks data loss.
34. **Three cache pathologies?** 🟢 Stampede (thundering herd), penetration (nonexistent keys), avalanche (mass expiry).
35. **Probabilistic early expiration?** 🟡 Recompute expiry as `exp − delta·rand()%K` to stagger natural expiry.
36. **What is a connection proxy?** 🟡 PgBouncer/ProxySQL multiplex thousands of client connections onto few backends.
37. **Postgres connection target per core?** 🟡 Roughly 2–4× cores, not "as many as possible."
38. **PgBouncer transaction-mode restriction?** 🟡 No session state: no LISTEN/NOTIFY across txns, temp tables, held advisory locks.
39. **Cross-shard uniqueness?** 🔴 Becomes unique-within-shard; needs a global index or application-level enforcement.
40. **What is a fan-out-on-read vs fan-out-on-write feed?** 🟡 Write = precompute inboxes (fast reads, expensive writes). Read = merge at read time.
41. **Hybrid fan-out threshold?** 🟡 Below ~1k followers, fan out on write; above, fan out on read (avoids viral write storms).
42. **Why not store images in the database?** 🟢 Blob storage has unbounded, cheap, CDN-frontable capacity; BLOBs don't.
43. **Bandwidth formula?** 🟢 QPS × payload size × amplification factor (never forget amplification).
44. **Replication bandwidth multiplier?** 🟡 1 write → every replica; 5 replicas ≈ 6× write bandwidth.
45. **Effect of compression on latency?** 🟡 gzip/brotli on 32 KB saves ~26 KB (~1 ms CPU) — worth it at any real bandwidth.
46. **Availability of 99.9% per month?** 🟢 43.2 seconds. (99.99% = 4.32 s; 99.999% = 0.43 s.)
47. **Nodes needed to tolerate f failures?** 🔴 2f+1. So 3 → 1 failure; 5 → 2 failures.
48. **Total availability of 99.99% app × 99.95% DB?** 🟡 0.9999 × 0.9995 = 99.94% — reliability compounds against you.
49. **RPO vs RTO?** 🟢 RPO = data you can lose. RTO = how long until you're back.
50. **Why are replicas not a DR plan?** 🟡 Replication protects against hardware failure, not deletion/corruption; you need tested backups + PITR.
51. **CAP precisely?** 🟢 Under a partition, choose C or A. (Not "you can't have both" — that only applies during a partition.)
52. **PACELC?** 🟡 If Partition → A or C; Else → Latency or Consistency. The E matters on every normal request.
53. **Cassandra/etcd — CP or AP?** 🟡 etcd is CP; Cassandra and DynamoDB are AP by default with conflict resolution.
54. **What is FLP?** 🔴 In an async system with one crash, deterministic consensus can't guarantee termination. Real systems use randomness + timeouts.
55. **Why does Raft use randomized election timeouts?** 🔴 To defeat FLP and resolve split votes without coordination.
56. **Raft's three safety rules?** 🔴 Election restriction · log matching property · leader completeness.
57. **When does a Raft leader commit an entry?** 🟡 When it's on a majority of servers (2f+1 quorum), not when all acknowledge.
58. **What breaks consensus?** 🔴 Process pauses: GC pause, VM freeze, VM migration, or a slow disk — you lose your leader lease.
59. **Split-brain vs flapping?** 🔴 Split-brain = two leaders, safety violation. Flapping = leadership oscillating, availability problem.
60. **Design a per-user session across 100 instances?** 🟢 Signed/encrypted cookie (≤4 KB) — no lookup, no dependency; or Redis with an instance-local cache and TTL.

---

# 12.1-12.4 SECTION READINESS CHECKLIST

Use this before you walk into a system design round. Every line is something a strong candidate
does *unprompted*. If you can't tick it, go back to the section named.

---

## A. Scalability Principles (12.1)

- [ ] I can name the **ladder of intervention** (fix query → cache → replicas → vertical →
      denormalize → connection proxy → partition → shard → new store → split service) and argue
      why sharding is last, not first. *(12.1)*
- [ ] I can state the **point of no return**: once sharded, no joins, no cross-shard
      transactions, painful rebalancing, and per-shard uniqueness. *(12.1.1)*
- [ ] I know **five load-balancing algorithms** and can say which I'd use and why — not just
      "round robin." *(12.1.2)*
- [ ] I can explain **liveness vs readiness vs startup probes** and why dependency health in
      liveness causes a cascade. *(12.1.2)*
- [ ] I know **outlier detection / passive ejection** and that it's better than health checks for
      partial failures. *(12.1.2)*
- [ ] I can enumerate the **three places state hides**: process memory, local filesystem, and
      implicit process identity. *(12.1.3)*
- [ ] I can list the **CDN cache directives that matter** and the two-request model; I can
      explain purge-by-tag vs versioned URLs. *(12.1.4)*
- [ ] I know the **five database scaling tools** and the failure mode of each (cache stampede,
      replica lag, connection explosion, partition pruning limits, cross-shard queries). *(12.1.5)*
- [ ] I can explain **modulo sharding's flaw**, consistent hashing, vnodes, and when HRW beats
      a ring. *(12.1.6)*
- [ ] I can recite the **microservices cost ledger** — especially that the value is
      organizational, not technical, and the number-one failure is chatty synchronous chains.
      *(12.1.7)*
- [ ] I can name the **right scaling metric per workload type** (queue depth for workers,
      concurrency for APIs, RPS for HTTP — not CPU for everything). *(12.1.8)*
- [ ] I can distinguish **reactive vs predictive** autoscaling and explain that cold start is
      why the first minutes of a spike are always lost. *(12.1.8)*
- [ ] I can define **backpressure vs load shedding**, name the patterns (bounded queues,
      priority shedding, deadline propagation, degradation ladder, random sampling), and know why
      **retry storms** amplify everything. *(12.1.9)*
- [ ] I can compare **active-active vs active-passive** and state the fundamental
      latency-vs-consistency trade across regions. *(12.1.10)*
- [ ] I know **cell-based architecture** and can say when single-region is still the right
      answer. *(12.1.10)*
- [ ] I can talk about **data residency** (regional cells, home-region routing, tokenization)
      and what GDPR actually changes in a design. *(12.1.10)*

## B. Estimation (12.2)

- [ ] I can produce from memory: **memory 100 ns, SSD 50–100 μs, spinning disk 1–10 ms,
      same-DC RPC 0.1–1 ms, cross-continent 70–150 ms, Redis GET ~1 ms, Postgres point
      query ~5 ms.** *(12.2.1)*
- [ ] I always state **percentiles with the QPS** ("1% of 10k QPS = 100 rps"). *(12.2.1)*
- [ ] I use **Little's Law** to convert latency into concurrency and connection counts. *(12.2.1)*
- [ ] I know typical **peak-to-average ratios** per product type (internal 1.2–2×, global
      consumer 2–4×, US-only 4–8×, event 10–50×). *(12.2.2)*
- [ ] I can convert **DAU → QPS** and then apply peak + headroom, in under 60 seconds, showing
      the arithmetic. *(12.2.2)*
- [ ] I break QPS down by **operation type** (read/write/search/media) and by read:write ratio.
      *(12.2.2)*
- [ ] I can estimate storage with **index + MVCC + replica multipliers (2–5×)** and I always
      separate **blob bytes from metadata bytes**. *(12.2.3)*
- [ ] I compute **bandwidth with an amplification factor** and cover both origin egress and
      user-facing bytes. *(12.2.4)*
- [ ] I can state the **nines table** with downtime per year/month/day, and can compute an
      **error budget**. *(12.2.5)*
- [ ] I know that **availability multiplies** across components and that **HA ≠ DR**. *(12.2.5)*
- [ ] I run the **5-step estimation script**: clarify → assume out loud → compute → sanity-check
      → attach a decision to each number. *(12.2.6)*
- [ ] I can do the **photo app** and **chat app** estimations end to end, and I'd notice if
      someone said "chat is read-heavy" — it isn't, at the storage layer. *(12.2.7)*

## C. Distributed Systems (12.3)

- [ ] I state CAP **precisely** (the choice exists only during a partition) and I know
      **PACELC** well enough that it's my default framing. *(12.3.1)*
- [ ] I can answer "is your design CP or AP?" **per operation**, and I know that AP without
      conflict resolution is just data loss. *(12.3.1)*
- [ ] I can list the **consistency models** in order and give a staleness budget per data path.
      *(12.3.2)*
- [ ] I distinguish **read consistency from write consistency** — the replica trap. *(12.3.2)*
- [ ] I can explain **Raft** in two minutes: 2f+1, leader + log replication, election
      restriction, log matching, leader completeness, and why reads use ReadIndex. *(12.3.3)*
- [ ] I know **FLP** and that **timeouts + randomness** are the escape hatch. *(12.3.3)*
- [ ] I know the practical Raft **operational landmines**: snapshotting, log compaction,
      membership change via joint consensus, read paths, and fsync-before-ack. *(12.3.3)*
- [ ] I can explain **leader election** mechanisms and know **ephemeral nodes + sessions** in
      etcd/ZK are the correct default. *(12.3.4)*
- [ ] I know **fencing tokens** and can explain why they make lease safety clock-independent.
      *(12.3.4)*
- [ ] I can implement the **idempotency key** pattern, including the same-transaction
      requirement, the request hash, and the response replay. *(12.3.5)*
- [ ] I know **which operations are and aren't naturally idempotent** (SET vs INCR, absolute
      write vs append). *(12.3.5)*
- [ ] I can explain the **transactional outbox** and why consumers must be idempotent. *(12.3.5)*
- [ ] I know why **2PC blocks** and when it's acceptable (one cluster you own). *(12.3.6)*
- [ ] I understand **compensation ≠ rollback** and that **irreversible steps go last**. *(12.3.6)*
- [ ] I can contrast **saga choreography vs orchestration** and give a decision rule
      (procedure → orchestration; independent reactions → choreography). *(12.3.7)*
- [ ] I know the saga **state machine**, reverse-order compensation, and that failed
      compensations need manual-intervention tooling. *(12.3.7)*
- [ ] I know **no global time exists**, can explain **Lamport vs vector clocks vs HLC**, and
      know when per-entity sequence numbers are the right answer. *(12.3.8)*
- [ ] I know the **lease problem** and can state the three conditions a lease needs. *(12.3.8)*
- [ ] I know **quorums**: R+W>N, why quorum writes are as slow as the slowest replica, and that
      real systems use **sloppy quorum + read repair + anti-entropy**. *(12.3.9)*
- [ ] I can compare **gossip (membership/health)** vs **consensus (safety-critical decisions)**,
      and know what **suspicion** prevents. *(12.3.10)*
- [ ] I can specify **timeouts (deadline propagation, not per-hop), retries (jitter, budget,
      one layer, idempotent only), circuit breakers (per dependency, slow-call aware), and
      hedging (p95 trigger, capped by load)**. *(12.3.11)*
- [ ] I can explain **bulkheads** and can name a real cascading failure they prevent. *(12.3.11)*
- [ ] I can specify a **distributed rate limiter**: token bucket, hierarchical limits,
      cost-based weighting, local + global layers, 429 + Retry-After. *(12.3.12)*
- [ ] I can list **named failure modes** — stampede, penetration, avalanche, gray failure,
      thundering herd, retry storm, cascading failure, poison pill, zombie process, brownout,
      clock skew, backpressure failure, tail amplification. *(12.3.13)*
- [ ] I can produce the **"what happens during a partition" table** for my own design, per
      operation. *(12.3.13)*
- [ ] I know what **CRDTs** guarantee (convergence) and what they don't (business invariants),
      and where LWW / OT / CRDT each belong. *(12.3.14)*

## D. The Interview Framework (12.4)

- [ ] I can run the **7 steps** in order and keep to the time budget. *(12.4)*
- [ ] I ask about **non-functional requirements** — latency, availability, consistency, cost,
      deletion, compliance — before designing anything. *(12.4.1)*
- [ ] I **scope in and out explicitly** and say why. *(12.4.1)*
- [ ] Every **estimate has an architectural consequence** attached to it. *(12.4.2)*
- [ ] My API has **cursor pagination, an error taxonomy, idempotency keys, optimistic
      concurrency, and a stated consistency model**. *(12.4.3)*
- [ ] My data model names **shard key + the query it serves**, indexes, ownership, and
      retention. *(12.4.4)*
- [ ] I can do at least one **deep dive with a latency budget per hop**. *(12.4.5)*
- [ ] I can name the **actual bottleneck** and quantify it, and I list **SPOFs** including the
      DB primary and the cache. *(12.4.6)*
- [ ] Every external dependency in my design has **a timeout, a circuit breaker, and a
      bulkhead**. *(12.4.6)*
- [ ] I have a **degradation ladder**: what drops first, second, third. *(12.4.6)*
- [ ] I can produce **three trade-off sentences** in the form "X over Y because Z, and the cost
      is W." *(12.4.7)*
- [ ] I have an **alternatives table** and a **10× trajectory**. *(12.4.7)*
- [ ] I narrate with structure, draw once and annotate, and handle **interruptions** by
      stopping, answering, and returning. *(12.4.8)*
- [ ] I handle **unknown questions** with acknowledge → reason → bound → offer, without
      bluffing. *(12.4.9)*
- [ ] I know the **scoring rubric** and can self-assess against it for my target level.
      *(12.4.10)*

---

## Level calibration — where the bar actually is

| Level | Must demonstrate | Common failure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Mid (3–5 yrs)** | Clarifies scope; basic estimation (QPS + storage); straightforward layered design; names caching and load balancing; identifies obvious SPOFs; clear communication | Trying to sound senior and stumbles on fundamentals; boxes and arrows with no depth |
| **Senior (5–8 yrs)** | Unprompted consistency, peak traffic, and failure discussion; justified shard key; timeouts/retries/breakers/idempotency; migration path off the riskiest decision; 10× trajectory; handles interruption | No migration path; treats failures as abstract; no cost or ops awareness |
