| **Staff/Principal (8+ yrs)** | Reframes around the real business constraint (cost/user, compliance, liability); whole-system reasoning (regions, cells, cost, orgs, on-call); simplification; business-level trade-offs; names risks and de-risks them experimentally; drives the agenda | Over-engineering; technology advocacy; no simplification |

## The 12 things to say unprompted (memorize this list)

1. "How stale is acceptable?" — the question nobody asks you to ask.
2. "Average QPS is X; peak is Y with a 2× safety factor."
3. "Images go in object storage, not the database."
4. "Idempotency key on anything that charges money or sends a message."
5. "Every external call gets a timeout, a circuit breaker, and a bulkhead."
6. "My shard key is chosen by the dominant query, and here's the query it makes slow."
7. "This read is bounded-stale at 30 seconds; this write is strongly consistent."
8. "Here's the SPOF: the database primary. Here's how I remove it."
9. "If this dependency is slow rather than down, here's what degrades."
10. "Here's my degradation ladder — what I drop first."
11. "I chose X over Y because Z; the cost is W."
12. "Here's the migration path if this decision is wrong."

## Final pre-interview drill

Do these three, out loud, on a whiteboard, in 12 minutes total:
1. **Photo app estimate** — QPS (avg/peak), storage split into blob vs metadata, bandwidth, and
   three architectural consequences. *(Drills 1–3)*
2. **Name the failure modes** for a design with: CDN → LB → API → service → Redis → Postgres →
   Kafka → S3. For each: what breaks, how it's detected, what the user sees. *(Drills F1–F12)*
3. **State your readiness check** — pick three items from the list above you still can't do
   fluently, and re-read those subsections. This is the highest-value 10 minutes of prep.

---

**Phase 12, Part 1 complete.** You should now be able to run a full system design interview:
clarify, estimate with visible assumptions, design an API and data model, deep-dive the critical
path with latency budgets, audit bottlenecks and SPOFs, and articulate trade-offs with a
migration path. Part 2 covers the domain-specific designs (URL shortener, news feed, chat,
video streaming, file storage, ticket booking, ride-hailing, rate limiter, and more), plus
capacity planning, cost modeling, and the behavioral/system-design round.

# PHASE 12, PART 2: Classic System Design Case Studies

> Twenty canonical system design questions. For each: the literal question, real requirements, real arithmetic, real APIs, real data models, real failure modes, real trade-offs.
> Every section follows the same 11-part skeleton so you can rehearse against a fixed template.

Difficulty legend used throughout:
- 🟢 Beginner · **Nice-to-know** — you can fake this, but expect it in phone screens
- 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — a real round-2 system design question
- 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — differentiating signal, expect deep follow-ups
- ⚫ Expert · **Deep dive** — staff/principal level, systems that only work at scale

---

## 12.5.1 URL shortener

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design a URL shortener like bit.ly. Users submit a long URL, we return a short one; when someone hits the short URL they get redirected. Walk me through the whole system."

What the interviewer is actually scoring:
- Can you produce unique short codes without a coordination service? (base62 encoding of a distributed ID, or pre-generated key pool)
- Do you realize the read path is ~500× hotter than the write path and must be cache-first?
- Do you know when to compute the 301 vs 302 and how expiry interacts with caches?
- Do you discuss the link-invalidation/abuse problem, or only the happy path?

Bad answer signature: "I'll use a database with an auto-increment ID and base62-encode it." That is the *easy* version. The differentiator is the read path, the skew, and the abuse surface.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. `POST /urls` with `{longUrl, customAlias?, expiryAt?, userId?}` returns `{shortUrl, shortCode}`.
- F2. `GET /{code}` → 301/302 to the destination, ideally without a hard dependency on the origin URL server.
- F3. Optional custom aliases, first-come-first-served, globally unique.
- F4. Optional expiry, optional click analytics (count, country, referrer, timestamp, rough geo).
- F5. Optional authenticated ownership: list my links, delete my link, rename my alias.
- F6. Optional deep-link: preview title/favicon by fetching the target page (async).

Non-functional
- N1. Write path: ~1k creations/sec peak, easy.
- N2. Read path: 10k–500k reads/sec for a mid-size product; assume **1M QPS** for a Twitter-scale campaign link.
- N3. Redirect latency budget: **< 20 ms p50, < 80 ms p99** end-to-end, from click to HTTP response headers. Redirect target fetch is out of scope and must be async.
- N4. Availability: **99.95%** (4 nines is defensible; a redirect outage is very visible).
- N5. Durability: a link created must survive forever (or until expiry) — RPO ≈ 0, RTO ~15 min.
- N6. Geo: point read from any region < 50 ms.
- N7. Security: no open-redirect abuse beyond user intent, block `javascript:`/`data:` schemes, malware-phishing detection, blocklists.
- N8. Cost: read path must be served from cache/CDN tier — the DB should see < 2% of read traffic.

Explicit non-goals: deep content analysis, ad insertion (bit.ly's business), account management.

---

**3. Estimation**

Write path
- 10M users, 3 links/user/month → 30M links/month.
- 30M / 2.592M s ≈ **11.6 writes/sec average**.
- Peak factor 6× (product launches, tweet storms) → **~70 writes/sec**. Even 10× headroom → 700 writes/sec. Trivial.

Read path
- 30M links/month, each clicked 4×/month → 120M redirects/month.
- 120M / 2.592M s ≈ **46 reads/sec average** for this product size.
- A viral link: 5% of a 1M DAU base = 50k users in 10 minutes → **83 reads/sec** average but with 10× burst at peak → **~1k RPS burst**.
- To make the exercise meaningful, state the assumption: "let's design for 1M RPS read, which is what a flash-crowd on a celebrity tweet looks like." At 1M RPS × 1KB response ≈ **1 GB/s egress** (not 1 MB/s — this is the number people miss). At 1M RPS the cost drivers are egress and cache hit rate, not CPU.

Storage
Per link record:
```
shortCode      7 B   (base62, 7 chars ≈ 3.5e12 keys)
longUrl        ~500 B average, cap at 2048 B
userId         16 B (uuid)
createdAt      4 B
expiryAt       4 B (nullable)
clicks         8 B
status         1 B
overhead/index ~120 B
```
- Say 800 B average. 3B links over 4 years.
- 3e9 × 800 B = **2.4 TB** raw → **~3 TB** with indexes and MVCC overhead.
- Click events: if we store one row per click, 120M clicks/month × 100 B = **12 GB/month**, 1.5 TB/yr. Fine in a columnar store, painful in row-store with high write contention. → Use an append-only columnar table + rollups.

Bandwidth
- Redirect response: 301/302 headers only, ~250 B. 1M RPS × 250 B = **250 MB/s egress** = 2 Gbps. Cheap.
- Analytics ingestion: 1M RPS × 100 B = 100 MB/s = 800 Mbps. Non-trivial. Batch/kafka.
- TTL: no need to fetch the destination synchronously. If we did page previews at read time, this becomes the dominant cost — always precompute.

Availability math
- 99.95% monthly downtime budget = 30 × 24 × 0.05% = **21.6 minutes/month**.
- With 3 AZs × N instances and one AZ failing (99.5% of all incidents historically): losing 1/3 of capacity must still serve ~1M RPS. → Per-AZ capacity ≥ 1.5M RPS, or degrade to cache-only.

Arithmetic to show aloud
- "base62 with 6 chars gives 62⁶ = 56.8B keys, 7 chars gives **3.52 trillion**. At 1k writes/sec we exhaust 3.52e12 keys in 3.52e9 seconds ≈ **111 years**. So 7 chars is safe forever; 6 chars lasts 567 days. That arithmetic is the whole key-length decision."

---

**4. API design**

`POST /v1/urls`
```json
// request
{
  "longUrl": "https://example.com/a/very/long/path?utm_source=x&utm_medium=y",
  "customAlias": "q3-launch",        // optional
  "expiryAt": "2026-12-31T00:00:00Z", // optional, null = never
  "password": "base64-or-null",        // optional
  "requireLogin": false
}

// 201 Created
{
  "shortUrl": "https://sho.rt/q3-launc",   // note: derived code, not sequential
  "shortCode": "q3-launc",
  "createdAt": "2026-10-03T09:14:22Z",
  "expiresAt": "2026-12-31T00:00:00Z"
}
```

`GET /v1/urls/{code}`
- 301 (permanent, cacheable, never revalidate) for never-expiring links.
- 302 (temporary, not cached) when `expiryAt` is set, `requireLogin` is true, or a password is set — a cached 301 would outlive the link.
- 404 unknown/expired. 410 Gone is arguably more correct for expired; interviewers love this nuance — mention it.

`GET /v1/urls?owner={userId}&limit=50&cursor=...`
- keyset pagination on `(user_id, created_at DESC, id DESC)`. Never OFFSET.
```json
{ "items": [ { "shortCode": "...", "longUrl": "...", "clicks": 143, "createdAt": "..." } ],
  "nextCursor": "MTc2MDAx..." }
```

`DELETE /v1/urls/{code}` → 204; we tombstone rather than delete so the code never gets reused.

`GET /v1/urls/{code}/stats?from=&to=&granularity=day` → 200 with a time series; served from the rollup table, never the raw table.

Error cases
| Case | Code | Body |
|---|---|---|
| Alias taken | 409 | `{"error":"alias_unavailable","retryAfterMs":0}` |
| Blocked URL (phishing/malware) | 422 | `{"error":"url_blocked","reason":"malware_scan"}` |
| Unsupported scheme | 400 | `{"error":"invalid_scheme","allowed":["http","https"]}` |
| Over length cap | 400 | `{"error":"url_too_long","maxBytes":2048}` |
| Expiry in the past | 400 | `{"error":"invalid_expiry"}` |
| Not owner | 403 | `{"error":"forbidden"}` |
| Rate limited | 429 | `{"error":"rate_limited","retryAfterMs":2000,"headers":{...}}` |
| Store unavailable | 503 | `{"error":"degraded_write"}` — **writes may be rejected while reads keep working** |

Idempotency: `POST` accepts `Idempotency-Key`; replays return the original code, not a new one. Cheap to add, shows maturity.

---

**5. Data model**

Primary store (key-value / wide-column, sharded by `shortCode`).

```
links
  PK  short_code      TEXT(7)      -- base62, "q3-laun" style
      long_url        TEXT(2048)
      user_id         UUID NULL
      status          ENUM(active, tombstoned)
      created_at      TIMESTAMP
      expires_at      TIMESTAMP NULL
      expires_ts      BIGINT NULL    -- monotonic, TTL policy uses this
      click_count     BIGINT DEFAULT 0
      requires_auth   BOOL
      password_hash   BYTES NULL
  INDEX (user_id, created_at DESC, short_code)   -- my links list
  INDEX (expires_at) WHERE status='active'        -- sweeper
```

Secondary: analytics (columnar, append-only)
```
click_events
  ts           TIMESTAMP
  short_code   STRING
  geo_country  STRING(2)
  referrer     STRING
  user_agent_hash STRING    -- hashed, not raw; PII
  edge_pop     STRING
PARTITION BY (day, hash(short_code))
TTL 90 days
```

Rollups
```
click_daily
  day, short_code, count          -- 1 row per active link per day
click_hourly_global
  hour, count                     -- never fan out the hot-key case
```

Why sharded KV instead of Postgres: the read key is the hot key, write volume is tiny, and we want a TTL/eviction-friendly cache in front. Postgres with a `TEXT` PK is fine at 1k writes/sec — don't oversell the need for NoSQL. Good answer: "Postgres is genuinely sufficient here; I'd shard it when it hurts. The read path is where the real architecture lives."

Access patterns
| Pattern | Served by |
|---|---|
| Redirect by code | CDN edge → L1 → L2 → KV → Postgres |
| List my links | Postgres index on (user_id, created_at) |
| Click count | Atomic counter in KV, flushed async |
| Stats chart | Columnar rollup |
| Expire sweep | Range scan on expires_ts, every 60 s |

Key insight for the hot-key problem: a celebrity link is 40% of all traffic on a single key. That key must be in every edge cache. Mitigations: (a) promote hot keys to a dedicated in-memory shard replicated to all edges, (b) put the click counter behind a sharded counter so the hot key isn't also a write hotspot, (c) never read-through to the DB for a key with traffic.

---

**6. High-level design**

Components
```
Client
  |
  +--> Edge PoP (CDN worker)  -- GET /{code}: L1 cache lookup, 95%+ hit
  |        | miss
  |        v
  |     Regional Cache Tier (Redis/memcached cluster, 2 replicas per AZ)
  |        | miss
  |        v
  |     Link Service (stateless, K8s, HPA on QPS)
  |        |
  |        +--> Link KV store (sharded by short_code)
  |        +--> Postgres (durable master, per-AZ replica, +1 read replica)
  |        +--> Analytics producer (Kafka topic click-events)
  |
  +--> Write path: API Gateway -> Write Service -> Key Allocator -> Postgres
                                  |                |
                                  |                +--> Key Pool (Redis sorted set of pre-generated codes)
                                  +--> URL Safety Scanner (async, marks status=pending|blocked)
                                  +--> Preview Fetcher (async: title, favicon, screenshot)
                                  +--> Analytics consumer -> columnar sink -> rollups
```

The request path for a read, precisely
1. Browser hits `GET https://sho.rt/q3-laun`.
2. Edge PoP worker computes cache key `link:v1:q3-laun`. Hit (95%+) → emit `301 Location: <longUrl>`, add `Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable`, record a sampled hit counter, return. Total: ~5 ms, zero backend calls.
3. Miss → regional cache. Hit (4%) → same, `max-age=60`, return.
4. Miss → Link Service. It does an in-process LRU of 10k entries first.
5. Miss → Link KV by `short_code`. Hit (≈0.9% of traffic) → repopulate all cache tiers, return 302 (safe default), and asynchronously increment the click counter.
6. Miss → Postgres. On hit, write-through to KV and both caches, return.
7. Not found / tombstoned / expired → negative-cache a `410 Gone` for 30 s (prevents DB hammering from scanners) and return.

Diagram worth drawing: edge → cache → service → KV → Postgres, with an async tap off the service into Kafka for analytics. That is 5 boxes and it is the whole picture.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Key generation: three designs, pick one and defend it**

| Approach | Mechanism | Collision handling | Coordination | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A. Auto-increment + base62 | DB id, `encode(62, id)` | impossible by construction | central writer | correct, becomes the bottleneck at ~50k ids/sec on one master, and leaks volume |
| B. Hash + collision probe | `base62(crc/fnv(longUrl))`, on PK conflict increment and retry | retry loop | none | simple; but same URL→same code is arguably a feature, and adversarial inputs cause clusters |
| C. Pre-generated key pool | Offline workers mint random 7-char base62 codes, push into a Redis sorted set / DynamoDB GSI | verified unique before insertion | none at request time | best: request path is a single LPOP/SREM, writes scale horizontally, keys are random so no enumeration |

Choose C. The write path becomes: `LPOP key_pool` → if empty, run mint worker → insert row. Explain why random keys matter: with sequential IDs, an attacker can enumerate the entire database and, more importantly, inflate the popularity of early links (link squatting). With `hash` you can also mount a chosen-prefix attack to make short codes spell words — cute but a red herring; mention only if asked.

Counter-argument you should raise yourself: random 7-char keys mean codes are not compressible and could theoretically collide with a user's requested alias. Mitigation: reserve a namespace — aliases live in a separate table, resolved before the KV lookup, and the mint worker checks the alias table before inserting.

**7.2 301 vs 302 — and the browser cache trap**

- 301 is cached by browsers **permanently** and aggressively, forever, with no revalidation. Once a user clicks a bit.ly link that 301s, that browser will never see the destination change even if you later retarget the link.
- That is what you want for "this alias is mine forever." It also means link rotation/retargeting is impossible for those users.
- 302 is not cached (or only heuristically). Required when: `expiresAt` set, `requireLogin`, password present, or when the operator wants to be able to change the destination.
- Third option, the honest one: **307/308** semantics with `Cache-Control: max-age=<remaining TTL>` so the edge caches aggressively but the entry disappears when the link does.
- Security trap: a user-created short link to `https://evil.com` **is** an open redirector, and it will get your domain blacklisted by email clients and chat apps. Hence the scanner and blocklist. Also emit `X-Robots-Tag: noindex` and a `rel="noopener noreferrer"`-friendly interstitial for untrusted users' links if you care about SEO abuse.

**7.3 The read path: how you actually hit 1M RPS**

Layered caches, and the important number is the *aggregate* hit rate.

- Edge PoP: 200 locations. Each holds top-N hot keys. Hit rate 90–95% because working set is tiny (top 1M codes ≈ a few hundred MB).
- Regional L1 (in-process LRU, 10k entries, 256 MB): 3% additional.
- Regional L2 (Redis, 3 AZ × 2 replicas, 64 GB each): 1.5% additional.
- Total cache hit ≈ **99.5%**. That leaves **5k RPS** to the Link Service, which 30 pods handle trivially (each pod does 170 RPS).
- This is the punchline: *the DB sees 5k QPS at 1M QPS of client traffic.* Say it out loud.

Write-through vs write-back on the read-through miss: write-through to L2 and L1, and to the edge via a purge/refresh API. Cache invalidation here is trivial because links are **write-once** — no update path. Say that; it is why this design is easy and chat is not.

Negative caching is mandatory: bot traffic will hit `/zzzzzzzz` at a rate comparable to real traffic. A 30-second negative TTL on 404/410 collapses that.

**7.4 Custom aliases and the first-come-first-served race**

`POST /urls {customAlias: "launch"}` from 50k users in the same second. You cannot rely on "INSERT and catch the duplicate" alone if you also need to serve a cache-friendly read path, because the cache is the source of truth for reads.

Design:
1. Alias reservation = unique index on `aliases(alias)`. Winner inserts; losers get 409.
2. On winner commit, publish `alias-created:{alias}` to an invalidation channel; every cache tier drops the key. Cache-fill races are bounded: read-through populates only *after* verifying in the KV, and the KV write happens after the DB commit.
3. **The dangerous window**: a cache entry populated by a stale negative-cache entry created before the alias existed. Fix: negative TTL of 5–15 s for *unknown aliases* (not 30 s), plus an explicit invalidate on creation.
4. Alternative that removes the race entirely: serve alias reads from a strongly-consistent store (Redis with the alias as key and the row as value), accepting one extra hop. Only needed if you promise sub-second alias availability.

Also: alias policy — min 3 chars, reserved word list (`api`, `admin`, `static`, `assets`, every TLD), rate limit per user (e.g. 10/day), case-insensitive by lowercasing everything (prevents `Q3-La` and `q3-la` from being distinct, which is a phishing vector).

**7.5 Analytics without destroying the redirect path**

The trap: incrementing a counter in the redirect path adds a write to the hottest path in the system. At 1M RPS, one counter is one key taking 1M writes/sec — a single-threaded problem.

Design:
- Redirect path emits at most a **sampled** event (1% of hits) or pushes to a local in-memory ring buffer flushed every 250 ms. Zero synchronous writes.
- Click *count* shown on the link detail page is eventually consistent and computed as: `exact_counter (Redis, sharded) + rollup_table`. Accept ±1% drift and say so.
- Sharded counters: `click:{code}:{shard}` with `shard = rand(0..63)`, summed on read. Turns 1M writes/sec on one key into 1M/64 = 15.6k writes/sec on 64 keys.
- Hot-key alternative: if a single link is getting > 5k RPS, switch that link's counter to in-memory per-pod counters aggregated via a periodic push. This is a *known-unknowns* point that interviewers reward.
- Never store raw IP or full user agent; hash/rotate. Retention 90 days raw, 2 years aggregated.

**7.6 Abuse, safety, and the compliance tail**

- URL reputation check on write: Google Safe Browsing / VirusTotal, plus a local heuristic (shortener-to-shortener chains, IDN homographs, `bit.ly/xyz` inside another short link).
- Status lifecycle: `pending` (scanning) → `active` or `blocked`. Reads on `pending` return 404 or a "being checked" interstitial — do not redirect unverified links.
- Blocklist sync (FBI/ICANN takedown lists) consumed from Kafka into an in-memory set consulted at the read path; must be < 5 ms.
- Per-IP and per-user creation rate limits; captcha past a threshold; ban scores in Redis.
- GDPR/right-to-be-forgotten: tombstone `longUrl` for a user-requested deletion but keep an HMAC of it so abuse investigations still work.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

What breaks first, in order
1. **The single hot key.** At ~100k RPS on one code, one Redis shard is saturated. Breaks before any capacity limit.
2. **Egress at the edge.** 1M RPS × 250 B = 250 MB/s across 200 PoPs; cost scales linearly and shows up in the bill before the CPU graph does anything interesting.
3. **Cache hit rate erosion.** New long-tail codes have cold keys. A site with 3B links cannot have a hot set that fits in memory; the hit rate falls from 99.5% to ~95% and your DB QPS goes from 5k to 50k. At that point sharding the KV, adding a read-replica tier, and Bloom filters become mandatory.
4. **Analytics ingestion lag.** Kafka retention pressure and rollup job duration; the dashboard shows stale numbers and someone files a bug.
5. **Postgres write contention** on the `links` table — only relevant past ~5k writes/sec sustained, which for this product is years away. Do not pretend it is the first wall.
6. **Expiry sweeper** scanning `expires_ts` — a range scan over 3B rows every 60 s. Fix with partitioning by time bucket so the sweeper touches one partition.
7. **Key pool exhaustion.** Mint worker throughput < write QPS → `LPOP` returns nil → latency spike and errors. Alert on pool depth; pool must hold ≥ 10× daily creation volume.

Concrete scale thresholds to state
- < 10k links: single Postgres, one cache. Over-engineering is a wrong answer here.
- 10M–1B links: Postgres sharded by `hash(code) % 16`, Redis cluster, 3 AZ.
- > 1B links / 1M RPS: KV store + edge PoPs + global anycast, analytics decoupled entirely.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Detection | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|---|
| Regional Redis down | health check, hit-rate metric collapses | serve from edge cache only; edge TTL keeps working | L2 refills as edges repopulate; no data loss |
| Postgres primary down | failover controller ( Patroni/SRE-graded) | **writes rejected with 503 `degraded_write`**; reads still work from cache/KV | promote replica, ≤ 60 s p99 write error burst |
| Link Service pod crash | k8s liveness | in-flight requests fail; retry at edge | restarts in seconds; stateless so no drain complexity |
| Whole AZ loss | NOC / synthetic from 3 regions | capacity reduced 33%; autoscaler adds in other AZs in ~3 min | traffic split |
| Bad deploy (redirect loop / wrong status code) | canary + synthetic monitor hitting known codes | 5% of traffic affected, auto-rollback | < 60 s |
| Cache stampede on a celebrity link | origin QPS spike vs baseline | thundering herd on KV | request coalescing + jittered TTL + single-flight |
| Analytics consumer down | consumer lag > 5 min | redirect unaffected; stats stale by lag amount | lag drains, backfill from Kafka |
| Blocklist feed stale | feed heartbeat age > 1 h | known-bad links remain servable | alert only; not user-visible |
| Key pool empty | pool depth metric | 429/503 on writes only | mint workers scale out; adds ~1 min of latency to create |
| Clock skew breaks TTL | NTP offset metric | links expire early/late by the skew amount | NTP; use monotonic `expires_ts` from the DB, not app clocks |
| Poisoned cache entry | canary on known-good codes | one code serves wrong URL for ≤ TTL | purge API |

The graded degradation ladder, stated explicitly: **edge cache → L1 → L2 → KV → DB → fail**. Each tier is optional above the one below it. The design never *fails closed* for reads; it fails *closed* for writes only when durability is at risk. That asymmetry is the correct policy for a redirector.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Code source | pre-generated random pool | DB auto-increment | no central writer, no volume leak, no enumeration attack |
| Redirect status | 301 for permanent, 307/302 for expiring | always 302 | 301 gives edge/browser caching for free; but breaks link rotation |
| Store | KV + Postgres (Postgres = source of truth) | Postgres only | avoids hot-shard writes, enables TTL; but adds a component |
| Cache strategy | cache-aside, write-once data | write-through on write path | writes are 500× rarer; cache-aside keeps the write path to one DB insert |
| Analytics | async sampling, rollups | synchronous counter increment on read | never put a write on the hottest path in the system |
| Expiry | TTL in cache + lazy check in service + sweeper | sweeper only | sweeper alone leaves a window where expired links still redirect |
| Alias namespace | separate table + invalidation | shared table | lets you reserve words and TLD-like strings without polluting code minting |
| Analytics storage | columnar with rollups | row store | 1M events/sec of append-only data is not a row-store workload |
| Geo strategy | anycast/regional edge with global replication | single-region + CDN | a redirect service with a 200 ms RTT is useless; users notice |
| Idempotent writes | `Idempotency-Key` honored | new code on retry | duplicate links from client retries are a real support burden |

Rejected whole designs worth naming
- **Kafka in the redirect path**: rejected. A redirect must be one hop, not a log append. Adds tail latency and makes the redirect depend on broker health.
- **Serving redirects from a CDN redirect rule table**: attractive (Cloudflare Workers / Fastly), but the rule table has a hard size limit and update latency. Good for the top 10k codes, not 3B.
- **GraphQL gateway in front**: rejected, no aggregation benefit for a single-key lookup.
- **ZooKeeper-backed ID allocation**: rejected, ZooKeeper is the wrong tool for a 1k/sec key mint.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "A single link is getting 40% of your traffic. Walk me through exactly what you do."**
Model answer: The code is already in every edge PoP's hot set, so 95% of hits terminate at the edge and never touch my infrastructure — that part is fine. The problem is the click counter and the analytics event. I shard the counter 64 ways (`click:{code}:{shard}`), so the hot key becomes 64 keys and 40% of 1M RPS becomes 400k writes/sec spread over 64 shards = 6.2k writes/sec per shard, which a single Redis shard handles. I also switch that specific code to per-pod in-memory counters flushed every second, so the write path drops to 64 in-flight increments. If the link exceeds 5M RPS I add a dedicated counter service for that code only, fronted by consistent hashing on the code so it scales horizontally. I never let the counter write become synchronous in the redirect. And I'd add a probe: the hot key is the *only* key with `origin_fetches/sec > 1000`, so my alert fires on it.

**Q2. "Why not just use a hash of the long URL as the code? It's deterministic."**
Model answer: Determinism is genuinely nice — same URL, same link, free idempotency, and the write path needs no coordination at all. The problems are three. One, base62 of a 32-bit hash gives only 4.3B keys, so you get collisions at scale and your retry loop becomes a birthday problem: with 3B links you are past the point where collision resolution is rare. Use a wider digest and you get 12+ chars, defeating the purpose. Two, a deterministic hash is trivially enumerable by anyone who knows your algorithm, which makes the whole keyspace crawlable — I'd have to rate limit and that hurts legitimate crawlers. Three, adversarial collisions: an attacker can grind URLs that land on the same code and overwrite a popular link, or deliberately DoS a specific code by forcing a long probe chain. I'd use hashing only for *idempotency* (a secondary unique index on `url_hash`) and keep the *code* random. Best of both: dedupe without enumeration.

**Q3. "How do you handle link expiry without a scan over billions of rows?"**
Model answer: Four independent mechanisms, each cheap. One, the cache entry has a TTL equal to the remaining lifetime — once the code falls out of edge and L2, it can't be served without a backend round trip. Two, the Link Service does a lazy check: on a read-through miss it compares `now` to `expires_ts` and returns 410 if past. Three, a sweeper partitioned by time — I bucket rows into daily partitions by `expires_ts`, so the 60-second sweep scans one partition, not the table, and processes at most a few million rows. Four, a coarse negative cache on the code itself for 5–15 seconds after expiry so a viral post-expiry crawl doesn't reach the DB. The cache TTL does 95% of the work; the rest is defense in depth. The key point is that TTL in the cache is what makes this *eventually* correct — the sweeper is just an optimization that makes it prompt.

**Q4. "Your DB is down. What happens, and what's the RTO?"**
Model answer: Reads keep working because the edge and L2 caches are independently durable — a 24-hour default TTL means most traffic never reaches the DB, so I serve from cache and simply don't write through. Writes are rejected with 503 and a `degraded_write` error, deliberately: I will not acknowledge a link I cannot durably store, because a lost link is worse than a failed create and the client can retry. RTO is the Patroni failover time, historically 30–60 seconds, because I run a synchronous replica in a second AZ for durability and an async replica for capacity. RPO is zero for acknowledged writes thanks to the synchronous replica. Once promoted, the Link Service drains its connection pool, and I explicitly do *not* fail back automatically — automatic failback after an incident is how you turn a 45-second outage into a 45-minute one.

**Q5. "How would you add a 'link preview' — title, favicon, image — without hurting create latency?"**
Model answer: Fully asynchronous, and it changes nothing about the redirect path. On create, I insert with `status=active` immediately and publish `link-created` to Kafka. A preview worker pool fetches the HTML, extracts `<title>` and `og:image`, downloads the favicon to S3, and optionally screenshots with headless Chrome. It writes to a separate `link_previews` table and pokes a cache invalidation. Redirect reads join that in one cache line so a missing preview costs nothing. The important constraints: fetch with a 3-second timeout, 5-redirect limit, 1 MB response cap, and never follow a link to a private IP range (SSRF — this is the thing an interviewer is actually probing for; block RFC1918, link-local, and `169.254.169.254` cloud metadata). Preview results are cached for 7 days and re-fetched lazily. Failed previews get exponential backoff, 3 attempts, then a permanent "unavailable".

**Q6. "How would you detect and stop someone abusing the service to launder phishing links?"**
Model answer: Layered, and biased toward failing safe on writes while failing open on reads for already-vetted content. Reputation on write: check the destination against a commercial URL-reputation feed plus my own heuristics — shortener chains, homograph/IDN domains, newly registered domains, credential-harvesting form patterns. Anything suspicious goes to `status=pending` and does not redirect. Abuse signals on the edge: burst creation rate per IP/subnet, single-IP fan-out, destination diversity per key, and low click-through on the destination. Those feed a ban score in Redis checked at the write path. Global blocklists from ICANN and law enforcement arrive over Kafka into an in-memory set consulted at read time in under 5 ms. And I keep a full audit log — creator identity, IP hash, timestamp, destination — because when a link does get reported, the first question is always "who made this," and if I can't answer it in under an hour the takedown process collapses. Rate limits are token-bucket per IP and per user, with the burst layer at the CDN so abusive traffic never reaches my compute.

---

## 12.5.2 Pastebin

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design a Pastebin. Users write text, get a URL back, and other people read it. Paste content can be large, can expire, and can be public, unlisted, or private."

What separates a strong answer from a weak one: pastebin is the *first* system where content size becomes a real architecture constraint (a 10 MB paste is not a database row), where the anonymous write path is an abuse problem before it is a feature, and where the read:write ratio inverts between anonymous and logged-in use. Most candidates design a CRUD app and miss all three.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. `POST /pastes` → `{url, id, rawUrl?}`. Anonymous allowed; logged-in gets ownership.
- F2. Syntax highlighting hint stored per language (optional, async).
- F3. Visibility: `public` (listed, browsable), `unlisted` (only via URL), `private` (requires login + ownership).
- F4. Optional password → auth prompt page.
- F5. Optional expiry (10 min → 10 years, presets).
- F6. Optional burn-after-reading / single-view.
- F7. Optional edit-after-create (unlisted only), with edit history.
- F8. Raw view (`/raw`) and download.
- F9. Optional clone/fork.

Non-functional
- N1. Paste sizes: text up to **1 MB** by default, hard cap 10 MB. This is the constraint that shapes everything.
- N2. Read-heavy: reads are 100–1,000× writes. Anonymous creation is easy; anonymous read is a scraper magnet.
- N3. Latency: create < 200 ms p99 (including upload of 1 MB), render < 100 ms p99, raw < 50 ms from CDN.
- N4. Availability 99.9%.
- N5. Durability RPO ≈ 0 for active pastes, 24 h acceptable for expired.
- N6. XSS must be structurally impossible — content is untrusted and rendered in the site's origin.
- N7. Cost: 90% of bytes served must come from object storage + CDN, not from app servers.

Non-goals: comments, real-time collab, versioning beyond edits.

---

**3. Estimation**

Write path
- 5M DAU, 4% post a paste per session → 200k pastes/day ≈ **2.3 writes/sec average**.
- Peak 10× → 23 writes/sec. Still trivial.
- Write volume in bytes: 200k/day × average 2 KB = **400 MB/day**, ~146 GB/yr. Trivial for object storage.

Read path
- 200k pastes/day × 40 reads = 8M reads/day ≈ **93 reads/sec average**.
- A single paste shared into a big community: 100k reads in an hour = **28 reads/sec**, burst ~200 RPS.
- Top 1% of pastes get 60% of reads → hot-set skew, and those go in CDN with long TTL.

Storage
- 1% of pastes > 100 KB. If average is 2 KB, the tail dominates volume:
  - 200k/day × 0.99 × 2 KB = 396 MB/day
  - 200k/day × 0.01 × 200 KB = 40 MB/day (wait, recompute: 2000 × 200 KB = 400 MB/day)
  - So the 1% tail is ~50% of bytes. **Average is a liar for storage sizing.** Say this.
- At 800 MB/day → 292 GB/yr → 5-year retention = **1.46 TB**. Plus versioning/multipart overhead → ~2 TB.
- Metadata row: ~300 B. 200k/day × 300 B = **60 MB/day** = 22 GB/yr. Fits in Postgres forever.
- The metadata fits in Postgres; the bytes do not. That split is the design.

Bandwidth
- Reads: 8M/day × 2 KB avg, but skewed — call it 8M × 12 KB (skew-adjusted) = **96 GB/day egress** ≈ 1.1 MB/s average, with peaks of ~50 MB/s. At $0.08/GB that is ~$7.7k/month. The number people forget: egress cost is the dominant unit economic of a pastebin, not storage.
- Upload: 400 MB/day in, 16 GB/month. Trivial.

Availability
- 99.9% monthly budget = **43.8 minutes/month**.
- Reads should degrade to CDN-only. Writes can degrade to "reject new pastes" if the store is unavailable; anonymous users get less than logged-in users under load — an explicit, documented prioritization policy.

Arithmetic
- "Store 1 MB in Postgres and you get an average row size of ~2 KB, which means the buffer pool holds only 12k rows and every `SELECT` is a heap fetch. Store it in S3 and your metadata table's average row is 300 B, so the buffer pool holds 1.7M rows and it's entirely in memory. One decision, an 800× difference in working set."

---

**4. API design**

`POST /v1/pastes`
```json
// request (application/json, or multipart for >64KB)
{
  "content": "def f():\n    return 42",
  "language": "python",            // optional, hint only
  "visibility": "unlisted",         // public | unlisted | private
  "password": "s3cret",            // optional
  "expiresIn": "7d",               // 10m | 1h | 1d | 7d | 30d | never
  "burnAfterRead": false,
  "title": "my snippet"
}
// 201 Created
{
  "url": "https://paste.io/a/Kj3nQr",
  "id": "Kj3nQr",
  "rawUrl": "https://paste.io/raw/Kj3nQr",
  "visibility": "unlisted",
  "expiresAt": "2026-10-10T09:14:22Z",
  "bytes": 27
}
```

`GET /v1/pastes/{id}` → metadata + rendered payload reference
```json
{
  "id": "Kj3nQr",
  "title": "my snippet",
  "language": "python",
  "visibility": "unlisted",
  "bytes": 27,
  "createdAt": "2026-10-03T09:14:22Z",
  "expiresAt": "2026-10-10T09:14:22Z",
  "edits": 1,
  "contentUrl": "https://cdn.paste.io/content/v3/Kj3nQr/9f2a..."   // signed, short TTL
}
```
Returning a **signed content URL** rather than inline bytes is the key API decision: it moves a 1 MB payload out of the JSON response, lets the CDN do the work, and lets you rotate content versions by changing the path.

`GET /raw/{id}` → `text/plain; charset=utf-8`, `Content-Disposition`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`.

`GET /v1/pastes/{id}/auth` → the password interstitial.

`PUT /v1/pastes/{id}` → edit (unlisted + owner only). Bumps `version`, writes new S3 object, appends to edit history.

`DELETE /v1/pastes/{id}` → 204 (tombstone, then async GC of the object).

`GET /v1/pastes/public?cursor=&language=&limit=50` → the public listing.

Error cases
| Case | Code | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Body over cap | 413 | `{"error":"too_large","maxBytes":10485760}` |
| Binary / non-UTF8 | 415 | reject, never sniff |
| XSS-inducing content | 200 but escaped | never reject, always escape |
| Expired | 410 | |
| Burn-after-read, already read | 410 | |
| Wrong password | 401 + `Retry-After: 3` | rate-limited brute force |
| Private, not owner | 403 | must be **403 not 404 only**, but consider 404 to avoid existence leaks |
| Raw disabled for private | 403 | |
| Rate limited (anonymous) | 429 | much stricter than logged-in |

Rate limits worth stating: anonymous 10 pastes/hour/IP, logged-in 100/hour, plus a global daily byte budget per IP class. And a hard rule: **anonymous and authenticated are served by different capacity pools**, so a scraper flood cannot evict paying users.

---

**5. Data model**

Metadata (Postgres — small rows, relational queries, real indexes)
```
pastes
  id            TEXT(8) PRIMARY KEY       -- base62 random, NOT sequential
  owner_id      UUID NULL                 -- NULL for anonymous
  visibility    ENUM(public, unlisted, private)
  password_hash BYTES NULL
  burn_after_read BOOL DEFAULT false
  title         TEXT(200) NULL
  language      TEXT(32) NULL
  byte_size     INT
  content_sha   BYTES(32)                 -- dedupe + integrity
  s3_key        TEXT NULL                 -- NULL for tiny pastes (inline mode)
  inline_content TEXT NULL                -- < 4 KB lives here, avoids an S3 round trip
  status        ENUM(active, burned, deleted)
  view_count    BIGINT DEFAULT 0
  created_at    TIMESTAMPTZ
  updated_at    TIMESTAMPTZ
  expires_at    TIMESTAMPTZ NULL
  version       INT DEFAULT 1
  INDEX (owner_id, created_at DESC, id)
  INDEX (expires_at) WHERE status='active' AND expires_at IS NOT NULL
  INDEX (visibility, created_at DESC, id) WHERE visibility='public'
```
Hybrid storage (row below 4 KB in Postgres, above in S3) is a genuinely good answer — it removes the S3 dependency for the 99% case. Name the trade-off: two read paths to maintain and test.

Content (S3, versioned by content hash)
```
s3://paste-content/v{version}/{id}/{sha256[0:2]}/{sha256}
  Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable
  Content-Type: text/plain; charset=utf-8
  x-amz-server-side-encryption: AES256
```
Immutable + content-addressed = **cache invalidation is free**. Edits create a new key; nothing is ever mutated.

Hot/counter store (Redis)
```
rc:{id}          → view counter, sharded 16 ways
rate:{ip}:{hour} → token bucket
throttle:anon    → global anonymous throughput limiter
```

Data model decisions to defend
- **Random IDs, not sequential.** Sequential IDs mean `/p/1`, `/p/2` … are all enumerable, so the entire archive is crawlable and "unlisted" means nothing. This is the single most important decision in the design and the one most candidates miss.
- **`text` not `bytea`/binary** for Postgres content, and never trust `Content-Type` from the client — sniff and force `text/plain`.
- **`expires_at` as a real column with an index**, not TTL-only, so you can answer "what expires soon" and enforce deletion order.

Access patterns
| Pattern | Served by |
|---|---|
| Read paste | Postgres meta → S3/CDN content (or inline) |
| Raw fetch | CDN only |
| My pastes | `owner_id` index |
| Public listing | partial index on `visibility='public'` |
| Expire sweep | partial index on `expires_at`, partitioned by month |
| Burn-after-read | `UPDATE ... WHERE status='active'` row lock, returns 1 row or 0 |

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Client (browser, curl, API)
  |
  +--> CDN / Edge
  |      static assets, /raw/* (long TTL, immutable)
  |      cache GET /a/{id} HTML shell (60s)
  |
  +--> API Gateway (rate limit: anon pool vs auth pool)
         |
         +--> Paste Service (stateless, autoscaled)
         |      +--> Postgres (primary + 1 sync replica + read replicas)
         |      +--> S3 (content >= 4 KB)
         |      +--> Redis (counters, throttle)
         |      +--> Kafka (paste-events)
         |              +--> Expiry Worker (partitioned sweeper -> S3 lifecycle + row delete)
         |              +--> Abuse Scanner (URLs, PII, malware regexes -> status=blocked)
         |              +--> Syntax/Preview Worker (optional: highlight language detection)
         |              +--> Analytics (columnar)
         |
         +--> Renderer (optional) Headless Chrome -> static HTML snapshot for CDN
```

Request path for a read
1. CDN miss → API Gateway → Paste Service.
2. `SELECT` metadata by `id` (PK, sub-millisecond, in buffer pool).
3. Auth check: `private` requires session + owner; `password` requires a matching header/cookie.
4. Status/expiry check. Expired → 410 + cache the 410 for 60 s.
5. If `inline_content` present → return it.
6. Else mint a short-TTL signed S3/CDN URL (or serve a pre-rendered static snapshot) and 302, or stream from S3 if the client set `Accept: text/plain`.
7. Async: increment sharded view counter, emit an analytics event (sampled).

The optional static-snapshot renderer is a great talking point: pre-rendering `/a/{id}` to HTML and serving it from the CDN makes the common read path *entirely* static, so you can absorb a 100k-read viral paste without touching a single backend.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Storage split: when does content stop being a database row?**

Decision table by size, which you can defend out loud:

| Paste size | Storage | Rationale |
|---|---|---|
| < 4 KB | Postgres `inline_content` | avoids a second system for the 99% case; row still < 8 KB so no TOAST |
| 4 KB – 1 MB | S3, inline in the API response for the first read, then cached | no |
| > 1 MB | S3 only, response is a redirect | prevents a 1 MB JSON body from pinning a server connection |

The real reason to move bytes out of the database is **not** capacity, it is these four things, and you should list them because interviewers push back on "just use S3":
1. Connection/heap pressure — a 10 MB row means every full scan and every `SELECT *` risks TOAST detoasting and a big memory spike.
2. Backup/restore time — restoring 1.4 TB of blobs is a different operational problem from restoring 22 GB of metadata.
3. Replication lag — large values mean WAL amplification and lagging replicas, so a read replica serves stale pastes.
4. Cache efficiency — metadata rows are small and stay in the buffer pool; blobs never do, so they will always miss.

But also name the cost of going S3-only: two-phase failure (metadata exists, object doesn't), no atomic transaction across both, and one more dependency in the create path. Hence the hybrid.

**7.2 XSS: the vulnerability this design must be structurally immune to**

A paste rendered inside your origin that executes script is stored XSS with full access to your cookies and session. The correct answer is *not* "we escape output" — it's "untrusted content never renders in our origin."

Layers, in order of preference:
1. **Serve all user content from a separate origin** (`pasteusercontent.io`) with `sandbox`, `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'`, and no cookies. This is the only defense that is actually complete.
2. If it must be same-origin: server-side templating with contextual auto-escaping, plus a `default-src 'none'` CSP on the paste page, plus `sandbox` iframes for any rich rendering.
3. `text/plain` and `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` on `/raw`, so a browser will never sniff a paste into HTML.
4. **Never** run `innerHTML` with paste content; if you syntax-highlight on the client, use a highlighter that builds DOM nodes from tokens (Prism/Highlight.js emit token *spans* from parsed output) rather than injecting a pre-rendered HTML string from an untrusted source.
5. Strip or escape `<script>`, event handlers, and `javascript:` URLs server-side as a *second* layer, and scan for it in the async abuse scanner so you can retroactively block an already-created paste.
6. Store the content as `text/plain` and mark the whole paste object `nosniff`.

Explicitly say: HTML pastes rendered in a sandboxed iframe with no `allow-same-origin` is the accepted residual risk, and that is a product decision, not an engineering one.

**7.3 Abuses: the anonymous write path is the attack surface**

Ranked list of how anonymous pastes get abused, with a countermeasure each:
1. **Malware/phishing hosting.** Countermeasure: URL + regex reputation scan, block executable/archive MIME, block `javascript:`, high entropy base64 blobs. `status=pending` until scanned.
2. **CSAM and illegal content.** This is the one that will kill a real product. Countermeasure: hash-based (perceptual + exact) CSAM detection in the async path, mandatory reporting, evidence-grade immutable audit log, and a documented legal-response SLA measured in minutes. Do not put this detection on the synchronous write path — enqueue and scan, but make sure the read path checks `status=blocked`.
3. **Data exfiltration.** Countermeasure: DLP regexes for API keys, private keys (`-----BEGIN ... PRIVATE KEY-----`), AWS keys, JWTs, credit-card numbers (Luhn), SSN patterns. Auto-redact on display, flag on write.
4. **Spam and SEO.** Countermeasure: unlisted-by-default for anonymous, cap pastes per IP, human-review queue for anything over a link threshold.
5. **Resource exhaustion (1 MB × 1M uploads).** Countermeasure: byte budget per IP per hour, not just a count; enforce the cap at the CDN/ALB layer with `Content-Length` inspection so oversized bodies are rejected before they consume bandwidth.
6. **Tor/anonymity isn't the threat; volume is.** Countermeasure: global anonymous throughput token bucket, so even distributed scrapers are capped.

The point to make: an anonymous create endpoint is a public file-hosting service. Design it as such.

**7.4 Expiry and lifecycle: four mechanisms, no full scans**

1. **S3 lifecycle rules** — the bytes die on a prefix schedule (`content/expired/`) — no app involvement, no cost, runs for free.
2. **Cache TTL** — the CDN and Redis entries carry `expires_at`; once evicted, the object is unreachable.
3. **Partitioned sweeper** — Postgres partitioned monthly on `created_at`, sweep each partition for `expires_at < now()`, delete rows. Cost is proportional to *expiring* rows, not total rows.
4. **Lazy expiry** — on any read, if `now > expires_at` then 410 and mark for deletion. This catches everything the other three miss and is what actually guarantees correctness.

Plus S3 multipart-upload cleanup: if a client dies mid-upload, incomplete multipart parts cost money forever. Add `AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload: 7 days` lifecycle. Mention it; it's a real production bug people have hit.

**7.5 Burn-after-reading: correctness under concurrency**

Burn-after-read means the read *and* the delete must be atomic, or two readers both see it.
```sql
UPDATE pastes
   SET status = 'burned', view_count = view_count + 1, burned_at = now()
 WHERE id = $1 AND status = 'active' AND (expires_at IS NULL OR expires_at > now())
RETURNING s3_key, inline_content, byte_size;
```
- One statement, so the row lock guarantees exactly one winner. `RETURNING` means you get the content and the burn in one round trip — no read-then-write race.
- Serving the bytes *after* the commit is safe because exactly one caller got the content back.
- Redis counter is then wrong-by-one relative to the DB; make the DB the source of truth for burns and let the counter be approximate elsewhere. State that trade-off explicitly.
- Add a `Cache-Control: no-store` on burn-after-read responses so a shared corporate proxy cannot serve it to the wrong person, and never CDN-cache it.

**7.6 Edits, history, and the immutability trade-off**

- `PUT` does not mutate; it writes `v{n+1}` to S3 under a new key and bumps `version`. Old versions are retained 30 days for rollback and are *not* publicly addressable.
- The public URL `/a/{id}` now serves changing content. Immutability is gone at the *page* level even though the *objects* are immutable.
- Consequence: CDN `Cache-Control` must drop to `max-age=30` for editable pastes, which costs cache hit rate. Cheaper alternative: the URL content-addressed — `/a/{id}/{version}` — and the edit response includes a new URL; the old URL keeps serving the old version forever. Call it out: this is a real product/engineering fork, and both answers are defensible if you name the trade-off.
- Anti-abuse: edits only for `unlisted`, only by owner, rate-limited, and the original is preserved (an edited paste that turns malicious must be traceable to its source).

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

What breaks first
1. **Egress cost at scale, not CPU.** 96 GB/day → 3.5 TB/month. The graph that goes up and to the right is the bill, not the CPU. Design response: aggressive CDN TTL on `/raw` (immutable, 1 year), and consider serving raw from object storage directly with a custom domain.
2. **A viral paste.** One paste, 200k reads in 10 minutes = 333 RPS of *identical* content. Answer: CDN snapshot or a pre-signed URL, plus a hot-key in Redis for the counter. This is the same skew lesson as the URL shortener, so cross-reference it.
3. **Anonymous write abuse** hits the create path *and* your bandwidth before it hits your compute. Enforce at the edge.
4. **Postgres table bloat** from expiry churn. 200k deletes/day on a 100M-row table; without partitioning, autovacuum falls behind and read latency degrades. Partition monthly.
5. **The public listing query.** `WHERE visibility='public' ORDER BY created_at DESC` on 100M rows without a partial index is a production incident waiting to happen. Partial index + keyset pagination, or move to a dedicated "recent public" Redis list maintained by the Kafka consumer.
6. **S3 request cost at small object scale.** 200k PUTs/day × $0.005/1000 = ~$30/month. Fine. But at 50M/day it's $8.3k/month, at which point the hybrid inline path for < 4 KB is a genuine cost win, not just a latency one.
7. **Abuse-scanner lag** during a spam wave → pending pastes queue up → new pastes delay their first read. Add backpressure.

Scale thresholds
- < 1M pastes: single Postgres, S3, Redis, one region, CDN. Do not shard.
- 1M–100M: monthly partitions, read replicas, snapshot renderer, abuse pipeline, multi-region CDN.
- > 100M: KV for metadata too, dedicated public-listing service backed by a feed, regional write + global read with async replication (metadata is read-heavy and last-write-per-field, so lag is tolerable for most fields — but *not* for `burned`, which is why burns route to the primary).

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Policy |
|---|---|---|
| Postgres primary down | reads continue from replica; **anonymous creates rejected with 503**, authenticated creates retried for up to 3 s | Explicit tiering of who gets service under failure |
| S3 unavailable | inline pastes (< 4 KB) still work; large pastes create returns 503 | Hybrid design pays off exactly here |
| S3 object missing but row exists | 410 Gone, log as `content_missing`, delete the row | Never 500 on a read — a missing blob is a permanent state |
| Redis down | counters fall back to DB increments; rate limiter fails **open** for authenticated, **closed** for anonymous | Anonymity is the resource being protected |
| Kafka down | expiry lag builds; nothing user-visible until it exceeds the retention window | Alert on lag; the sweeper is idempotent so drain is safe |
| Abuse scanner down | pending pastes never clear → they 404 | Fail closed for unverified content, fail open for verified |
| CDN cache poisoned / stale | stale content until TTL | Purge API + versioned keys make this rare |
| Client dies mid-upload | incomplete multipart parts | S3 lifecycle abort at 7 days; also cap by `Content-Length` at the edge |
| View-counter Redis hot key | counter drift | sharded counters + eventual reconciliation from Postgres |
| Scanner/limiter false positive on a legit paste | user blocked | appeal path + manual review; log the decision with model version for auditability |
| Region loss | metadata replicated, S3 multi-region | reads served from the surviving region; creates rejected or routed to the DR region |

Degradation ladder: **CDN snapshot → CDN cached response → read replica metadata + S3 → primary metadata → fail**. Note the asymmetry deliberately chosen: anonymous writes degrade before authenticated writes.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Content store | hybrid (inline < 4 KB, S3 above) | S3 only | removes a dependency for the majority of reads |
| Metadata store | Postgres | DynamoDB | you need listing, ownership queries, partial indexes, and transactional burn; relational wins |
| IDs | random base62 | sequential int | sequential makes `unlisted` meaningless |
| Edit model | new version, new key, old retained | in-place update | immutable objects ⇒ free cache invalidation |
| Page rendering | optional pre-rendered static snapshot | server render every read | makes viral reads free |
| Content origin | separate sandboxed origin | same origin | structural XSS immunity beats escaping |
| Auth tiering | separate anon/auth capacity pools | one shared pool | a scraper flood must not evict real users |
| Abuse detection | async scan with `pending` status | synchronous scan | synchronous scanning puts a third-party API on the write path |
| Passwords | bcrypt/argon2 hash | plaintext | obviously; also constant-time compare and rate-limited attempts |
| Password pastes | hashed + interstitial + rate limit | share links | share links leak via referrer headers; the interstitial must also set `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` |
| Expiry | S3 lifecycle + partitioned sweeper + lazy check | single TTL | defense in depth; the lazy check is what makes it *correct* |
| Public listing | dedicated feed service | query on Postgres | avoids a permanent hot sort over 100M rows |

Rejected wholesale
- **Elasticsearch for content search.** Adds operational weight and a reindex pipeline for a feature nobody asked for. If asked, say search is a separate service fed by Kafka, not a synchronous dependency.
- **Serving content directly from Postgres for all pastes.** Fails at ~10 MB pastes and makes backups brutal.
- **Trusting client-supplied language/syntax hints for rendering.** Interpreters are a security problem; detect language in the async worker and only highlight with a known-good, non-executing highlighter.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "Why random IDs instead of auto-increment? Seems wasteful."**
Model answer: Because "unlisted" is the product. With sequential IDs, `/p/1001` through `/p/999999` are all reachable by anyone incrementing a counter, which means unlisted pastes are actually public, private-paste existence is trivially enumerable, and view counts become predictable enough to target. Random 8-char base62 gives 62⁸ = 2.18 × 10¹⁴ keys; at 3 writes/sec average I exhaust that in 2.3 × 10⁶ years, so I never need to think about reuse. The "waste" is 8 bytes of randomness, and it buys me the entire privacy model of the product plus protection against anyone doing sequential scraping. I do lose insertion-ordered locality, but my primary access is a point lookup by PK, so there is no locality to lose.

**Q2. "A user pastes 8 MB. Walk me through exactly what happens."**
Model answer: The edge rejects it immediately if `Content-Length` exceeds 10 MB, with 413, before any body is read — that is the cheapest place to stop it. Between 4 KB and the cap, the gateway streams the body to a pre-signed multipart upload URL rather than buffering it in the app, so the app server never holds 8 MB of user data in heap. S3 takes the object; the API call to finalize contains only the metadata plus the `ETag`/checksum. I verify a SHA-256 client-computed in the request against S3's checksum and reject on mismatch, which catches truncation. Then I insert the metadata row with `status=active` and return a URL — the response never contains the content. On read, `byte_size > 1 MB` means the API issues a 302 to a short-TTL signed URL instead of proxying, so a 200k-read viral paste never touches my servers. If the client dies mid-upload, the incomplete multipart parts are cleaned by a 7-day lifecycle rule.

**Q3. "How do you prevent someone using your pastebin to host malware?"**
Model answer: Three gates, none of them on the synchronous critical path. On write, cheap synchronous checks: scheme allowlist, reject `javascript:` and `data:` URLs, reject archive and executable content types, and a URL-reputation lookup on any links present. Then the paste goes to `status=pending` and returns 201 with a "being checked" flag rather than a live URL — actually, for anonymous pastes I'll gate the *first read* on `status=active`, so nothing unverified is ever served. Async scanner does the heavy work: full DLP regex sweep, archive detection, entropy analysis, and a rendering sandbox that screenshots the paste and runs a classifier on the image to catch obfuscated phishing. Third gate is retroactive: reports arrive on a queue and flip status to `blocked`, purging the CDN and deleting the object. The residual risk is a determined attacker who re-submits after each detection, which is why the per-IP byte budget and the ban-score matter more than any single classifier.

**Q4. "How would you add 'search across pastes'?"**
Model answer: Asynchronously, never inline. A Kafka consumer reads `paste-events`, and an indexing service writes a document — id, title, first 8 KB of content, language, tags, createdAt, visibility — into Elasticsearch/OpenSearch with the `visibility=public` filter baked in as a query-time constraint *and* enforced at index time so unlisted content is not even in the index. That last point is the security answer: unlisted and private content must never be indexed at all, otherwise search becomes a bypass for the visibility model. Ranking is BM25 on the truncated content plus recency boost. For typo tolerance I add an edge-ngram analyzer on the title field only. Writes remain fast because the index is downstream; a paste becomes searchable within a second or two, which is fine. If I needed sub-second visibility I would add a read-your-writes via a short-lived "just indexed" overlay in Redis keyed by user. And I'd reject full-content indexing for large pastes — 8 MB of text in ES costs enormous heap for near-zero recall benefit.

**Q5. "Explain the burn-after-read semantics and how you'd test them."**
Model answer: Burn-after-read means at-most-once delivery. The implementation is a single `UPDATE ... WHERE status='active' RETURNING content`, so the row lock serializes concurrent readers and exactly one gets a row back; everyone else gets 410. Because the content comes back from the same statement, there is no read-then-write window. The response gets `Cache-Control: no-store` so an intermediary cannot replay it, and it is never written to the CDN. The counter is incremented in the same statement and Redis is treated as eventually-consistent decoration. To test it: run 1,000 concurrent GETs against one paste with `k6` and assert exactly one 200 and 999 410s — that is the test that finds the bug in every naive implementation. Then re-run with the S3 path to make sure nobody reordered the object fetch before the conditional update, and add a chaos test that kills the service between the UPDATE and the response, asserting the paste is burned-but-unserved, which is the correct at-most-once outcome.

**Q6. "What's your biggest cost center and how would you attack it?"**
Model answer: Egress. At 96 GB/day from reads I am paying roughly $7–8k/month, versus pennies for storage and modest compute. Three attacks. First, move the raw bytes to a domain served straight from object storage with long-lived `immutable` caching so the CDN's hit rate approaches 100% — my current 60-second TTL on the HTML shell is the leak. Second, exploit the skew: 1% of pastes generate 60% of reads, so those need near-zero origin cost, which the pre-rendered static snapshot achieves. Third, audit whether anonymous traffic deserves a quota at all — if 70% of egress comes from scrapers, a per-IP byte budget plus a higher rate for authenticated users both saves money and improves service for real users. I would also check the request-cost side: at 50M PUTs/day, per-request S3 pricing makes the inline-under-4-KB path a genuine dollar decision rather than just a latency optimization.

---

## 12.5.3 Distributed rate limiter

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design a rate limiter for our API. It's distributed, it must be accurate, and it can't be a bottleneck itself. Walk me through the algorithms and the failure modes."

This question is a trap for people who jump straight to Redis. The interviewer is scoring whether you know (a) there are three algorithm families with different accuracy/latency/cost profiles, (b) where the token lives when you're globally consistent, (c) what you *lose* when the limiter's backing store is down, and (d) that for most real traffic, an edge/geo-local approximation beats a globally exact one.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Per-endpoint limits: e.g. `POST /api/orders` at 10/min/user, `GET /api/search` at 60/min/user.
- F2. Per-tenant, per-IP, per-API-key, per-device limits.
- F3. Composite: a request consumes from *all* applicable limiters (user AND IP AND tenant-global).
- F4. Dynamic: limits change at runtime without a deploy (tiers, abuse quarantine).
- F5. Response contract: 429 + `Retry-After` + `X-RateLimit-Limit/Remaining/Reset` + which rule tripped.
- F6. Burst allowance (token bucket) and sustained rate (leaky bucket / sliding window).

Non-functional
- N1. Added latency budget: **< 1 ms p99** on the hot path. This constraint eliminates whole designs.
- N2. Accuracy: ±1 request at the boundary acceptable; never *systematically* over-admit.
- N3. Availability: must **fail open** when unavailable, or the whole API dies with it. A rate limiter that takes down the API is a worse outage.
- N4. Scale to millions of keys, tens of thousands of QPS.
- N5. No single point of failure; no single hot shard.
- N6. Multi-region: a user may be served from any PoP.
- N7. Cost per check: a Redis round trip is ~0.3–0.5 ms in-region. That is the entire unit budget.

Non-goals: billing metering (that needs exact counts, not sampled decisions).

---

**3. Estimation**

Traffic
- 20M DAU × 200 API requests/day = 4B requests/day.
- 4e9 / 86,400 ≈ **46k QPS average** on the limiter.
- Peak 4× → **185k QPS** of checks.
- Note: every rate-limited internal dependency multiplies this. Estimate from *dependencies*, not from users — 30 services × 2 limited calls each will dwarf client QPS.

Key cardinality
- 20M users, 200k API keys. 60% mobile traffic behind CGNAT: ~1.5M distinct client IPs/day for 12M users.
- Rate-limited keys = distinct (subject, route) pairs in a 60 s window. Assume 5 routes per subject → **120M live keys**.
- 120M × ~150 B = **18 GB** of Redis. Fits comfortably, so shard for throughput and blast radius, not memory.

Throughput arithmetic that drives the design
- One Redis shard (single-threaded) does ~100k simple ops/sec. 185k checks/sec needs **≥ 3 shards**; use 8 for headroom and rebalancing.
- One Lua script keeps a check to one round trip: 23k ops/sec/shard. Comfortable.
- Now the decisive calculation: a *globally ordered* check via Raft/Paxos has 2–20 ms RTT and one leader at ~10⁴ ops/sec. **185k/sec is impossible under strong global consistency.** That arithmetic is the entire justification for eventual consistency here, and it is the number most candidates never derive.

Latency
- Edge-local check: **~0.05 ms**, zero network.
- Regional Redis: **~0.5 ms p50, ~1.5 ms p99** with TLS and a cross-AZ hop.
- Global consistent store: **5–20 ms p99** — 10× over budget.
- Therefore: exact locally, approximate globally.

Availability budget
- 99.99% for the store, but treat its unavailability as a routine, *exercised* state, not an exception. The design must be reviewed for that state.

---

**4. API design**

Two surfaces: an in-process SDK (preferred) and an optional standalone admin service.

In-process
```python
result = limiter.check(
    subject="user:8f3c-...",      # user | apikey:<id> | ip:203.0.113.9 | tenant:acme
    route="POST:/v1/orders",
    cost=1,                        # weighted: a 10 MB upload costs 10
    now=time.monotonic(),          # injected for testability
)
# { "allowed": True, "limit": 10, "remaining": 7, "resetAt": 1767000000,
#   "retryAfterMs": 0, "policiesEvaluated": 3, "trippedPolicy": None, "mode": "strict" }
```

`PUT /v1/policies/{route}`
```json
{
  "route": "POST:/v1/orders",
  "algorithm": "token_bucket",
  "capacity": 10,
  "refillPerSec": 0.1667,
  "scope": "subject",
  "costPerRequest": 1,
  "failMode": "open",
  "onExceeded": "reject_429"
}
```
→ 200. Changes publish to a config bus and land within ~1 s (push + local cache + poll backstop), so the hot path never calls for policy.

`POST /v1/admin/quarantine` → `{subject, until, reason, budgetPenalty}` → 202. Quarantine is implemented as a rate of zero through the same path, so it needs no new machinery.

Rejection contract
```
HTTP/1.1 429 Too Many Requests
Retry-After: 12
X-RateLimit-Limit: 10
X-RateLimit-Remaining: 0
X-RateLimit-Reset: 1767000012
X-RateLimit-Policy: "user:8f3c:POST:/v1/orders"
{ "error": "rate_limited", "policy": "user:8f3c:POST:/v1/orders", "retryAfterMs": 12000 }
```
Returning *which* policy tripped is not cosmetic — otherwise every client treats every 429 as "the limit I don't know about."

Edge cases
| Case | Behavior |
|---|---|
| Route missing from policy config | fail open + alert; never fail closed on a config miss |
| Store unreachable | fail open for ordinary routes, fail closed for abuse routes (signup, OTP, coupon) |
| Clock skew across PoPs | use store-side time, never client or PoP clocks |
| No subject identity | fall back to IP; if unusable, a signed anonymous cookie |
| `cost > capacity` | reject immediately, do not overflow the bucket |
| Cardinality attack (random IDs) | cap distinct subjects per (route, IP); beyond cap apply IP rule only |
| Retry storm after 429 | precise `Retry-After`; client-side exponential backoff with full jitter |
| Quarantine expiry | TTL handles it; no cron, no cleanup job |

---

**5. Data model**

```
rb:{route}:{subject}                # token bucket
  tokens   FLOAT   # remaining
  ts       INT     # last refill, epoch ms
  TTL = 2 * (capacity / refillPerSec)   # self-cleaning when idle
```
Two fields, not four. On read: `tokens = min(cap, tokens + (now-ts)/1000*refill)`. The TTL *is* the eviction policy — an idle-enough key refills to capacity and then disappears for free. No janitor process is the reason a rate limiter can be cheap; this is the best detail to volunteer.

```
sw:{route}:{subject}                # sliding window log, only when exactness is required
  ZSET  member=requestId  score=epochMs
  TTL windowMs
```
Exact via `ZREMRANGEBYSCORE` + `ZCARD`. Cost: one entry per request, so a 60 s window at 185k RPS is 11M entries (~700 MB) and several times the CPU. Reserve for high-value endpoints.

```
gc:{bucket}:{epoch}                 # fixed-window global cap
  counter INT   TTL 60
```
Cheap circuit breaker to protect the system, not to be fair. The ±burst at the boundary is fine at this altitude.

```
pol:{route}:{tier}                  # policy cache, mirrored to every PoP
```

State placement
- **In-process:** a fraction of the budget as a local pre-filter; policies cached locally.
- **Regional Redis:** authoritative for that region.
- **Global:** a 1 s aggregation of regional counters, consulted *only* when the regional check is near its limit.

Access patterns
| Pattern | Cost |
|---|---|
| Check | 1 Lua round trip, O(1) |
| Composite same-slot | 1 round trip |
| Composite cross-slot (user + IP) | 2 round trips (sequential scripts, take min) |
| Near-limit global check | 1 extra hop, ~5% of traffic |
| Policy change | 1 write + push |

Redis Cluster slot problem: composite rules need multiple keys. Ranked solutions:
1. **Hash tags** (`rb:{u8f3c}:POST:/v1/orders`) so a user's keys share a slot, and one Lua script touching only that user's keys.
2. Separate sequential script calls for cross-slot rules (IP, tenant-global) and take the minimum — 2 round trips, still under 1 ms in-region.
3. A single Lua script calling `redis.call` on cross-slot keys — **rejected**, fails at runtime on Cluster.
4. One big Redis instance for everything — rejected, no headroom, no blast-radius isolation.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Client
  +--> CDN / WAF        L4+L7 volumetric limit, geo-based, ~0 network RTT
  +--> API Gateway      per-IP and per-API-key token bucket (memory + Redis)
  |        | 429 here, never deeper
  +--> Service
         1. local policy cache            (no network)
         2. local pre-filter bucket = 5% of global budget   (no network)
            -> allowed? proceed, zero Redis traffic
         3. regional Redis Lua (authoritative for this region)
            evaluates all same-slot rules in ONE round trip
         4. cross-slot rules (IP, tenant-global) = 1 more round trip
         5. if consumption > 50%: check globally aggregated counter (rare)
         6. on Redis error: fail open, or local static budget on abuse routes
```

The 5% pre-filter is what makes this scale: the overwhelming majority of requests are far from their limit and never leave the process. Redis QPS drops by roughly an order of magnitude, and, more importantly, p99 improves because the common case has no tail at all. Diagram: CDN → gateway → service (cache + pre-filter) → regional Redis → rare global hop.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 The algorithm families, with real numbers**

| Algorithm | Accuracy | State/read | Ops | Fails how |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fixed window counter | ±burst at boundary (can admit 2× in 1 s) | 1 int | 1 | worst exactly at the boundary |
| Sliding window log | exact | ZSET, 1 entry/request | 3 | memory + CPU blow up |
| Sliding window counter (interpolated) | ±1, no boundary burst | 2 ints | 2 | needs 2 buckets |
| **Token bucket** | burst = capacity, by design | 2 fields | 1 | never exceeds capacity |
| **Leaky bucket** | smooths output, no burst | queue depth | 2 | adds latency, sheds load |
| **GCRA** | exact, O(1) state | 1 float (TAT) | 1 | harder to explain |

The real answer: **token bucket for user-facing API limits** (clients retry and users double-click, so real traffic is bursty and a limiter without a burst allowance produces false positives), **GCRA or sliding-window counter when fairness matters**, **fixed window for cheap global caps**.

GCRA deserves specific mention as the best of both worlds: store only the theoretical arrival time. Allow if `now >= TAT - burstTolerance`; on accept set `TAT = max(now, TAT) + emissionInterval`. One float, O(1), mathematically a leaky bucket with burst tolerance. Use it for payment and auth endpoints where bursts should be prohibited.

**7.2 Global accuracy without a global round trip**

The crux. A user must not get 10× their limit by bouncing across regions.

1. Each region owns an authoritative counter per key.
2. A 1 s aggregator sums regional counters into a global key. Aggregating all 120M keys is impossible, so **only near-limit keys are aggregated** (consumption > 50%): the hot path emits a high-water event to Kafka, a consumer updates the global key. Global state is at most 1 s stale, and it costs nothing for the 95% of keys that are nowhere near their limit.
3. Check order: regional check → if regional consumption > 50%, check global.
4. Enforcement: allow iff `regionalUsed < regionalShare AND globalUsed < globalLimit`, with `regionalShare = globalLimit / regionCount`. Steady-state over-admission is bounded by what one client can fire in the aggregation window: 2–5 requests.
5. Degradation: if the global counter is stale or missing, **reduce** the regional budget rather than raising it. Under-admission is a support ticket; over-admission means the limit is not a limit.

State the accuracy contract honestly: "±5% within a 1 s window. That is abuse-grade accuracy. For billing-grade enforcement I would use single-region GCRA with region affinity for those users, and accept the routing constraint."

**7.3 Fail-open vs fail-closed, endpoint by endpoint**

| Class | Examples | On limiter outage |
|---|---|---|
| Abuse-sensitive | signup, login, password reset, OTP, coupon redeem, promo code, file upload | **fail closed** |
| Cost-sensitive | LLM inference, SMS, paid search, transcode triggers | **fail closed** or fall back to a static budget |
| Correctness-sensitive | payments, inventory decrement, trading | **fail closed** |
| Ordinary | reads, search, feeds | **fail open** |

The refinement that makes this a strong answer: "fail closed" should not mean "reject everything." Each instance already holds a 5% local bucket, so on Redis failure I *raise* that local bucket to be the sole limiter. The cluster then admits roughly the global limit — degrading into approximate global rate limiting rather than a blackout. For the abuse class, the local budget goes to zero for those endpoints only, and those endpoints return 503 with `Retry-After`. One safety property to state explicitly: never fail closed on an *unknown policy*, because that converts a config bug into a full outage.

**7.4 Hot keys and cardinality attacks**

Two distinct problems:
- **Hot key**: one legitimate client at 50k RPS saturates a single-threaded shard while others idle. Fixes: client-side batching in the SDK; the local pre-filter; and for tenant limits, shard the tenant bucket 64 ways (`rb:{tenant}:{0..63}`) selected by hash of the API key. That converts one 50k-write/sec key into 64 keys at 780/sec. Cost: per-API-key consistency instead of tenant-global consistency, so the tenant's effective limit has a few percent of slop.
- **Cardinality explosion**: an attacker with a million fake user IDs evicts everyone else from memory. Fixes: cap distinct subjects per (route, source IP) and fall back to the IP rule; a floor TTL so short-lived keys still expire; `maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru` on a **dedicated** instance so a flood cannot evict non-limiter data; and a non-evictable "recent consumption" summary key, because eviction resets counters and therefore *escapes* limits — a subtle bug worth volunteering.

**7.5 Client-side correctness: turning 429 into a retry storm**

- Return `Retry-After` always, computed properly: token bucket gives `retryAfter = (1 - tokens) / refillRate`.
- Client-side **token bucket with full jitter** (`sleep = random(0, Retry-After)`). Fixed delays synchronize retries and defeat the limiter.
- Honor `X-RateLimit-Remaining` and self-throttle before 0, which smooths load instead of oscillating at the boundary.
- Do not count 429s as server errors, do not page on them, keep them out of the latency histogram. A rise in 429 is a *client behavior* signal.
- Escalating backoff with full jitter, capped at 60 s.

**7.6 Limits are a product decision, not a number**

- Ship tiers: free 1k/day, paid 100k/day. One global limit is a support burden.
- Prefer a `budgetPenalty` that slows a legitimate heavy customer over a hard 429 — soft degradation plus a human decision beats a wall.
- Quarantine (rate 0 with a TTL) over IP bans or deletion: same code path, automatic expiry, reversible.
- Every policy change is an audited, revertible event, because "why is this tenant 429ing" gets asked at 2 a.m.
- **Roll out in shadow mode first.** Compute the decision, log it, compare to real traffic, then enforce. This is the single highest-value process recommendation in this section: it prevents a launch-day self-inflicted outage and it produces the false-positive data you need to set the numbers.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

What breaks first
1. **p99 latency, not throughput.** One in-region round trip is 0.3–0.5 ms; two plus TLS is 1.5–2 ms; add a cross-region hop for the global check and you blow the 1 ms budget. Fixes are the local pre-filter and the >50% gate, not a bigger Redis.
2. **A shard saturating on hot keys.** 8 shards is fine on average but one hot key pins one shard. Shard hot keys or batch client-side.
3. **Memory from cardinality, not throughput.** 120M keys = 18 GB; a cardinality attack multiplies that by 10×.
4. **Global aggregation lag** → under-admission (bad UX) or over-admission (no limit). Aggregator lag is a first-class SLI.
5. **Config propagation** to every PoP within 1 s: push-based invalidation with a poll backstop.
6. **The limiter becoming a dependency.** Once 500 services import it, a bad release is a fleet-wide outage. That is why the design leads with in-process and a dependency-free library.
7. **Clock skew** — compute limits only from store-side time.

Scale thresholds
- < 1k RPS, one region: in-process token buckets only; Redis optional. Saying so is a mark of judgment.
- 1k–50k RPS: Redis cluster + Lua + local pre-filter.
- 50k–500k RPS, multi-region: regional authoritative + near-limit global aggregation.
