- > 500k RPS: enforcement moves to the edge with locally cached policies and per-region budgets assigned by a control plane; the data plane becomes a counter push, not a counter read.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Detection | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|---|
| Redis node down | client errors, latency | replica failover 2–5 s; in-flight checks fail → fail open or local static budget | automatic (Sentinel/Cluster) |
| Redis region unreachable | regional error rate | that region enforces its local share → global under-admission | multi-region Redis, or accept and document |
| Global aggregator down | lag metric | near-limit users use the reduced regional budget | consumer restart, Kafka replay |
| Lua script error or WRONGTYPE | error-rate spike | fail open + alert; never ship a script that can throw | pin script hash, CI-test against a live cluster |
| Policy push fails | version-behind metric | stale policies | 5 s poll fallback |
| Clock skew | offset metric | wrong limits per PoP | NTP; store-side time only |
| Cardinality attack | distinct-keys/sec, keyspace events | per-IP subject cap engages, memory protected | auto-ban + LRU eviction |
| Limiter library bug | canary + 4xx anomaly | one service affected | rollback; library versioned independently |
| Kafka down | lag | global over-admission | drain later; documented accepted risk |
| Retry storm | 429 rate spike | self-reinforcing overload | client SDK jitter; server `Retry-After` precision |
| `maxmemory` hit | memory metric | LRU evicts limiter keys → **counters reset → fresh buckets for everyone** | raise maxmemory; understand eviction = under-admission = limit escape |

Degradation ladder: **local static budget → local policy cache → regional Redis → global counter → per-instance approximation**. Note that no rung means "no limiting" for the abuse class; that is deliberate.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Placement | in-process library, Redis-backed | standalone limiter service | a network hop on the hot path is unacceptable; also avoids a hard fleet-wide dependency |
| Algorithm | token bucket; GCRA on strict endpoints | sliding window log everywhere | O(1) state, no per-request memory |
| Accuracy | regional-exact, global ±few % | globally exact | 185k RPS of globally ordered checks is arithmetically impossible |
| Global check trigger | only above 50% consumption | every request | ~20× fewer global hops |
| Eviction | TTL self-cleaning + LRU | janitor cron | no operational surface |
| Outage policy | per-endpoint with local static fallback | global fail-open | some endpoints must stay protected |
| Identity | composite (user + IP + key + tenant) | single identity | no single identity is spoof-proof |
| Enforcement point | gateway for identity, service for business rules | gateway only | the gateway cannot know request cost or business context |
| Rollout | shadow mode, then enforce | enforce immediately | prevents self-inflicted outages |
| Quarantine | zero-rate policy with TTL | ban lists / deletion | one code path, auto-expiry, reversible |

Rejected wholesale
- **Consensus-replicated global counters.** Correct and ~1000× too slow: one leader's sequential log at ~10⁴ ops/sec and 2–20 ms p99. Cite the arithmetic.
- **Token bucket in the CDN with a synced global count.** CDN config sync is eventual and coarse; fine for L4 volumetric defense, wrong for per-user fairness.
- **Sliding window log on a high-QPS endpoint.** At 185k RPS, ZSET write amplification is the bottleneck before memory is.
- **Per-user DB counters.** 0.5–5 ms per call and a row-lock hotspot. Never.
- **Rejection sampling.** Statistically sound for aggregate protection, useless for per-tenant fairness.
- **Limiting retrospectively from access logs.** Always one window behind; you cannot block what you already allowed.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "Redis is down and everything is down. What do you do at 3 a.m.?"**
Model answer: Nothing, by design, and that is the argument. Every service's limiter is an in-process token bucket that already reserved 5% of the global budget and was consuming it in memory with no network calls. The moment Redis errors, the SDK flips to `degraded` mode and those local buckets become the *only* enforcement. With 200 instances at 5% each, the cluster still admits about the global limit, so the system degrades into approximate global rate limiting instead of shutting down. The exception is the abuse class — signup, login, OTP, coupon redeem, SMS send — where degraded mode sets that local budget to zero and returns 503 with `Retry-After`. I accept 30 seconds of unavailability on those endpoints rather than letting attackers walk in while my store is down. Runbook: confirm Sentinel is failing over, watch the 503 rate scoped to the abuse class, page only if failover exceeds 60 seconds. What I would not do is flip a global `failOpen: false` — that is a config change with no blast-radius limit.

**Q2. "How do you make a global limit accurate without adding latency?"**
Model answer: Split the limit into a regional share plus a global total, and only pay for the global check when it can change the answer. Each region has an authoritative bucket per key, and a local in-process counter tracks consumption. When regional consumption crosses 50% of the limit, the request emits a high-water event to Kafka *and* does one extra lookup of a globally aggregated counter that a consumer refreshes once per second. Below 50% — the overwhelming majority of traffic — there is no global hop at all. Enforcement is `regionalUsed < globalLimit/regions AND globalUsed < globalLimit`. Steady-state error is bounded by what one client can fire inside the aggregation window: one to five requests. If the global counter is stale or missing I lower the regional budget rather than raising it, because under-admission is a ticket and over-admission means the limiter is not limiting. Framing: abuse-grade accuracy, exact for its purpose; billing-grade would need single-region GCRA with region affinity for those users.

**Q3. "Sliding window log vs token bucket. When do you pick which?"**
Model answer: Token bucket when I want an explicit burst allowance, which is the default for user-facing APIs. It is O(1) state — one float and a timestamp — and it directly encodes "you may send 100 requests and refill at 10 per second," which is language a product manager understands. The behavior people dislike is that a client can spend its whole burst in a few milliseconds, and if the downstream is bursty — a connection pool, a database with 50 connections, a batch job — that burst is what hurts even though it is within contract. In that case I use the leaky-bucket or GCRA form, which enforces the *shape* of the traffic, not just the average. Sliding window log is for when fairness is the requirement at low rates: daily quotas where a user must not be able to move 10,000 requests into the last second, or anything contractual. It is exact because it counts real timestamps, but it costs one ZSET entry per request, which at 185k requests/sec is 11M live entries and several times the CPU. So: log for low-rate exactness, token bucket for bursty allowances, GCRA for smooth shape at O(1) cost.

**Q4. "One enterprise customer is doing 50k RPS and your Redis shard is on fire."**
Model answer: Three moves in order. First, the local pre-filter already absorbs much of it: each of that customer's gateway instances holds 5% of the budget locally, so 5% of those 50k requests never touch Redis — 2.5k RPS gone with no code change. Second, if that is not enough, shard the tenant bucket: `rb:{tenant-acme}:{shard}` with shard = hash(apiKey) mod 64. One 50k-write/sec key becomes 64 keys at 780 writes/sec, and the SDK picks the shard so all traffic for a given API key is consistent. The cost is per-API-key consistency rather than tenant-global consistency, so the tenant's effective limit carries a few percent of slop, which is fine for a tenant cap. Third, if the aggregate across all 64 shards still saturates, the counter leaves Redis entirely: per-instance in-memory counters push deltas every 100 ms to a stream, summed by a sharded aggregator. At that point I stop calling it rate limiting and call it a quota negotiation — the customer gets a dedicated cluster or a reserved connection pool, because a client that legitimately needs 50k RPS has outgrown a shared limiter. The general rule: hot keys are solved by sharding or by moving the counter off the request path, never by making the cluster bigger.

**Q5. "How do you pick the numbers? Someone proposed 1000 requests/minute."**
Model answer: Bottom-up from the resource, not top-down from a round number. Take the most expensive dependent on that endpoint and compute how many concurrent requests saturate it. A Postgres pool of 50 connections with 25 ms queries sustains 2,000 QPS; with a 4× safety factor for traffic mix and deploys, the endpoint's sustainable rate is about 500 QPS. Convert that to a per-user limit using the observed P99 request rate for that endpoint: if the heaviest real users send 20 requests/minute, a 100/minute cap is roughly 2.5× normal behavior, which catches scripted abuse without tripping a real user. Then validate by shadow mode for two weeks: log what *would* have been throttled and walk the false-positive list with the top twenty accounts. If a named account trips it, the number is wrong. Two more inputs: burst capacity should be roughly one page load's worth of requests, because browser retries and double-clicks produce real bursts; and there must be a separate hard global cap as a circuit breaker, so no misconfigured per-user policy can take down the database.

**Q6. "How would you roll this out to 500 services without an outage?"**
Model answer: Four stages, with the SDK enforcing rather than the services. Stage one, the library ships **no-op by default**: it returns allowed, costs ~200 ns, and records subject and route. Stage two, shadow mode everywhere — compute the decision, log it, return allowed. Run two weeks per endpoint and tune against the false-positive list. Stage three, enforce per service and per endpoint, canary first: 1% of instances behind a flag, with automatic rollback if the 429 rate for that service exceeds 0.1% or if the protected resource crosses its saturation threshold. Watch the *protected resource's* health, not the limiter's error rate — a limiter that throttles correctly while the database melts is still a failure. Stage four, tighten fail modes and document. Two hard rules: policies live in the config system services already use, so they roll back without a deploy; and the library is version-pinned centrally, because a buggy limiter in 500 services is a correlated outage and that is the failure mode I fear most.

---

## 12.5.4 Chat and messaging (WhatsApp-style)

⚫ Expert · **Deep dive**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design WhatsApp. One-to-one and group chat, online/offline status, delivery receipts, end-to-end encryption, and it has to work on flaky mobile networks."

This is the hardest system in the list. The interviewer is scoring whether you understand (a) the **three-way problem** — you cannot have strong consistency, high availability, and partition tolerance, and chat's answer is *availability first, eventual consistency*, (b) a persistent connection at scale is not "use WebSockets" but a session directory plus a fanout tier, (c) sync after offline is the real algorithm, and (d) encryption is a protocol design problem before it is a crypto problem.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. One-to-one and group conversations (up to ~1,024 members; broadcast channels for larger).
- F2. Send text, image, audio, video, document, location, contact, sticker.
- F3. Per-conversation message history with pagination in both directions.
- F4. Online/typing indicators, last seen.
- F5. Delivery receipts (sent / delivered / read) and read counts in groups.
- F6. Presence across multiple devices for one user account.
- F7. Offline queue: a message sent while offline is delivered when the device reconnects.
- F8. Multi-device: new device pulls full history and stays in sync.
- F9. End-to-end encryption by default.
- F10. Media upload with resumable, chunked transfer.
- F11. Push notifications (APNs/FCM) when the app is backgrounded.
- F12. Message edit, delete-for-everyone, reactions, replies/quotes, star.

Non-functional
- N1. Send latency: **< 300 ms p50** to delivery on a good network; **< 1 s p99** online.
- N2. Fanout: 40k RPS online sends peak for WhatsApp-scale (1B+ users).
- N3. Availability: **99.99%+**; chat is a utility and users notice instantly. Design for the weakest link being down.
- N4. Durability: an acknowledged message must not be lost. RPO = 0 from the sender's perspective, which means an **append-only, replicated log write before ACK**.
- N5. Message ordering: per-conversation, and monotonic per sender. Global ordering is neither possible nor required.
- N6. Connection count: **500M+ concurrent sockets**.
- N7. Sync on reconnect: a device offline 7 days must catch up without a 10 GB download.
- N8. Battery/data: idle connections must cost almost nothing.
- N9. Encryption: E2EE with forward secrecy; server holds no plaintext.
- N10. Attachments: up to 100 MB, uploaded directly to object storage via presigned URL, not through the chat service.

Non-goals: calls (WebRTC SFU), channels/stories, business messaging, payments.

---

**3. Estimation**

Writes
- 1B users, 60 messages/user/day → 60B messages/day.
- 60e9 / 86,400 ≈ **694k messages/sec average**; peak factor 3–4× → **~2.5M msg/sec peak** for the very largest deployment. For a mid-size product assume 40k peak RPS.
- Bytes: 60B/day × 160 B average (ciphertext + overhead) = **9.6 TB/day** of message metadata written; attachments dominate volume (say 12 KB average → 720 TB/day), which is why attachments go straight to object storage with the chat service only ever seeing the URL.

Reads / fanout
- Fanout is the crux. A group of 256 active members means one write → 256 delivery attempts. If 5% of a user's 60 messages/day go to groups averaging 100 members, group fanout adds 1B × 0.6 × ... let me do it cleanly: 60M group messages/day × 100 members average = **6B fanout operations/day = 69k fanout/sec average, ~250k/sec peak**.
- Online delivery is cheaper than that (only connected devices), but the *pull* path must exist for everyone else.

Connection math that makes or breaks the design
- 1B users, assume 15% concurrently connected = **150M sockets**.
- One gateway process with 100k connections → **1,500 gateway nodes**. At 60 connections/sec handshake capacity each, a 100k-connection ramp takes 28 minutes, so **rebalancing on failure must be gradual**, not instant — this is a hard operational constraint and a great detail to state.
- Idle cost: 150M × 64 B/s of keepalive overhead = 9.6 GB/s across the fleet. Use app-level pings at ~1/minute, not transport keepalives, and batch them across connections on a gateway.

Storage
- Metadata row per message: `conversation_id(16) + sender_id(16) + seq(8) + ciphertext(~200 B) + index fields(~40 B) + overhead` ≈ **300 B**.
- 60B/day × 300 B = **18 TB/day**, ~6.6 PB/year. At $0.08/GB hot, keeping 90 days hot costs 1.6 PB ≈ $130k/month — the dominant storage decision is therefore **tiering**: hot 7 days on SSD, warm 90 days on HDD, and cold older messages in cheap object storage with on-demand promotion.
- The key insight: at 60B messages/day, keeping *everything hot forever* is impossible, so **pagination must be served from a hierarchical index, not a `LIMIT/OFFSET` scan**.

Bandwidth
- 1B × 15% online × 40 B/s ≈ **6 GB/s egress** for connections plus payload. Fanout of 250k messages/sec × 300 B = **75 MB/s** through the delivery tier.
- Media is the elephant: 720 TB/day in and out. Direct-to-object-storage upload plus CDN-ish delivery keeps chat servers out of the media path entirely.

Availability math
- 99.99% = 4.3 minutes/month. With ~150M sockets across 3 regions, one region = 33% of connections. A single region loss must not lose a single acknowledged message: therefore **durability lives in the append-only log with cross-region replication, while presence lives in an ephemeral store that can be lost.** State that split explicitly — it is the core of the design.

Arithmetic worth showing
- Group fanout: 256-member group, 10 messages/sec in the group → 2,560 delivery/sec from one conversation. A single conversation is a **hot partition** no matter how you shard the database, because all writes for that conversation must be sequential. The only fix is to split the fanout from the append: the log write is sequential, the fanout is horizontal. That single sentence explains why every chat architecture looks the way it does.

---

**4. API design**

Transport: WebSocket for online sessions; HTTPS for everything else. One gateway protocol, versioned.

`WS /v1/connect?token=<jwt>&deviceId=...&since=<cursor>`
Server → client frames:
```json
{ "type": "msg", "conversationId": "c_9f3a", "seq": 48211, "senderId": "u_1a2b",
  "ciphertext": "Ct0Bl3...", "iv": "...", "senderKeyId": "k_7", "sentAt": 1767000000123 }
{ "type": "receipt", "conversationId": "c_9f3a", "seq": 48211, "state": "delivered" }
{ "type": "presence", "userId": "u_77", "state": "online", "lastSeen": 1767000000000 }
{ "type": "typing", "conversationId": "c_9f3a", "userId": "u_77", "isTyping": true }
{ "type": "sync_begin", "conversationId": "c_9f3a", "fromSeq": 40110, "count": 8101 }
```

Client → server:
```json
{ "type": "send", "clientMsgId": "c8f2-uuid", "conversationId": "c_9f3a",
  "ciphertext": "...", "iv": "...", "senderKeyId": "k_7",
  "expirationTimer": 604800 }
{ "type": "receipt", "conversationId": "c_9f3a", "seq": 48211, "state": "read" },
{ "type": "ack", "seq": 48211 },
{ "type": "sync_req", "conversationId": "c_9f3a", "fromSeq": 40110, "limit": 500 },
{ "type": "ping", "t": 1767000000000 }
```

`POST /v1/media/init` → `{uploadUrl, mediaId, expiresIn}` (presigned, direct to S3).
`POST /v1/media/commit` → `{mediaId, sha256, bytes}` → `{url, width, height, durationMs}` (async transcode/thumbnail, status polled via WS `media_ready`).
`GET /v1/conversations?cursor=&limit=50` → list with `lastSeq`, `unreadCount`, `pinnedUntil`.
`GET /v1/conversations/{id}/messages?beforeSeq=&limit=50` → **keyset pagination on `seq`, never offset**.
`PUT /v1/presence` → `{state, lastSeen}`; also driven by the WS frame.
`POST /v1/devices` → register a new device, returns a prekey bundle for E2EE bootstrap.
`GET /v1/keys/{userId}` → prekey bundle for sending an offline message.

Error cases
| Case | Code | Behavior |
|---|---|---|
| Invalid/expired token | WS close 4401 | client refreshes and reconnects; backoff |
| Client message already sent | idempotent | `clientMsgId` dedupe returns the original `seq`, no duplicate |
| Conversation not a member | 403 | and a `WELCOME` sync for conversations the client is entitled to |
| Seq gap detected by client | `sync_req` | client heals by pulling the gap; server never assumes the client's state |
| Message older than retention | 410 with `truncatedAtSeq` | client shows "messages unavailable" |
| Media too large | 413 | 100 MB cap |
| Rate limit | 429 | abuse/flood protection is a first-class concern (see 12.5.3) |
| Push token invalid | 202 + async unregister | never retry a dead token |

The `clientMsgId` idempotency key is the single most important field in the protocol. Mobile clients retry aggressively; without it you get duplicate messages, which in chat is a correctness bug users perceive as data corruption.

---

**5. Data model**

Message store — append-only, partitioned by `conversation_id`.
```
messages                       -- append-only, sharded by hash(conversation_id)
  conversation_id   BYTES(16)  PRIMARY KEY part 1
  seq               BIGINT      PRIMARY KEY part 2      -- per-conversation, monotonic
  sender_id         UUID
  ciphertext        BYTES(4096)
  iv                BYTES(16)
  sender_key_id     TEXT
  sent_at           TIMESTAMPTZ
  client_msg_id     UUID        UNIQUE (conversation_id, client_msg_id)
  expires_at        TIMESTAMPTZ NULL
  edited_at         TIMESTAMPTZ NULL
  deleted_at        TIMESTAMPTZ NULL
  reply_to_seq      BIGINT NULL
  reaction_summary  VARBINARY(512) NULL   -- 16 most common reactions only
  PRIMARY KEY (conversation_id, seq)
```
Why an append-only log with a composite PK:
- Every write to a conversation is sequential in `seq`, which is what makes ordering free.
- Range reads for sync are a single contiguous scan — `WHERE conversation_id=? AND seq BETWEEN ? AND ?` — over a small number of large sequential pages. No index fan-out.
- Deletes and edits are **tombstones**, not mutations, so the append path never contends with the read path.
- Reactions are an in-place summary on the message plus a bounded reaction table; unbounded per-reaction rows would turn every reaction into a write on the hot partition.

Hierarchical index for pagination (critical at 60B messages/day)
```
conversation_index
  conversation_id
  level        TINYINT          -- 0 = every 256 seqs, 1 = every 4096, 2 = every 65536
  bucket_start BIGINT
  byte_offset  BIGINT           -- pointer into the segment file
  INDEX (conversation_id, level, bucket_start)
```
Paging through history reads level-2 buckets to get offsets, then reads only the needed segments. Without this, "scroll to the bottom of a 4-year-old chat" is a table scan of hundreds of millions of rows.

Device/session store
```
devices
  device_id      UUID PRIMARY KEY
  user_id        UUID
  platform       ENUM(ios, android, web, desktop)
  push_token     TEXT
  last_seen_at   TIMESTAMPTZ
  prekeys        BYTES            -- consumed one-time, replenished by the client
  INDEX (user_id)
  INDEX (push_token)              -- for token invalidation sweeps
```

Presence (ephemeral, in Redis)
```
presence:{userId}          -> SET of deviceIds, TTL 90 s, refreshed by ping
last_seen:{userId}         -> epoch seconds, written on disconnect
presence:conn:{deviceId}   -> gatewayNodeId, TTL 90 s   (session directory)
```

Delivery receipts
```
receipts
  conversation_id, seq, device_id, state, updated_at
  PRIMARY KEY (conversation_id, seq, device_id)
```
Compacted: only the aggregate state per (conversation, seq, device) matters, and read counts roll up.

Fanout queue
```
Kafka topic `chat_fanout`, partitioned by conversation_id
key = conversation_id   -> ordering preserved per conversation
value = {conversation_id, seq, payload_ref}
```

Access patterns
| Pattern | Served by |
|---|---|
| Append message | log shard by `hash(conversation_id)` |
| Sync after offline | range scan on `(conversation_id, seq)` + hierarchical index |
| Deliver to online devices | fanout worker → session directory → gateway |
| Deliver to offline devices | per-user queue; on reconnect, batched pull from the log |
| Presence | Redis, 90 s TTL |
| Push | FCM/APNs batch, throttled/collapsed |
| "Unread count" | per-device `last_read_seq` counter, not a query |

`last_read_seq` per (conversation, device) is the correct unread design — never compute unread by scanning messages.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Client (mobile / web / desktop)
   |  WebSocket (one per device, multiple devices per user)
   v
[Global Anycast LB]
   v
Gateway Cluster (stateless, 1500 nodes, holds 100k sockets each)
   - session directory lookup in Redis (deviceId -> gatewayId)
   - local read buffer cache per conversation
   - app-level ping scheduler (1/min, batched)
   |
   +--> Send Path
   |      1. auth token + deviceId -> resolve userId, conversation membership (cached)
   |      2. dedupe on clientMsgId
   |      3. APPEND to log shard (conversation_id, seq)  <-- ACK only after this
   |      4. ACK to sender with seq
   |      5. publish `chat_fanout` to Kafka (key=conversation_id)
   |
   +--> Fanout Service (N workers, horizontal)
   |      for each member: resolve deviceIds -> gatewayId via Redis session directory
   |      batch per gateway, push; if offline -> enqueue per-user or rely on pull-sync
   |      aggregate receipts
   |
   +--> Sync Path (on reconnect / multi-device)
   |      client sends since-cursor -> range scan on (conversation_id, seq)
   |      using hierarchical index for old history
   |
   +--> Offline Delivery
   |      on device connect: batched pull from log (single query, ordered)
   |      push notifications only as a wake-up hint
   |
   +--> Media Service: presigned upload -> object storage -> async transcode
   +--> Presence Service: Redis with TTL
   +--> Push Service: FCM/APNs, collapsed per conversation
```

Read path for a delivered message: Kafka → fanout worker (1) session directory (Redis) (2) batch to gateway (3) socket. Three hops, no per-recipient service call. The batching per gateway is what makes 1B users feasible.

Diagram this as five lanes: sockets/gateways, message log (append-only), fanout (Kafka + workers), presence (Redis + TTL), sync (log range scan).

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Ordering without a distributed transaction**

Requirements: per-conversation ordering and monotonic per-sender. Global ordering is impossible and unnecessary.

Design: the log shard for a conversation is a single sequential sequence. `seq` is allocated by the shard owner, not by the client, so the client's clock is irrelevant (mobile clocks are wrong constantly — a real, commonly-missed detail). Two writers to the same conversation serialize on the shard; different conversations are independent. To reduce contention further, a conversation's sequence allocation is a local atomic increment plus an idempotent write keyed by `clientMsgId`, so a client retry produces the *same* `seq`.

Practical consequences you should state:
- Client-generated timestamps are display-only; `seq` is truth. Ordering in the UI uses `seq`.
- A late-arriving offline message gets the `seq` it was assigned at send time, so it appears in the correct historical position on reconnect — the reason `seq` is server-assigned and stored in the log.
- "Edit" and "delete" are tombstone events carrying a `seq` range, so they propagate in order too.
- No distributed lock, no two-phase commit, no global clock.

**7.2 The fanout tier: online push vs offline pull**

Two delivery modes and you must justify the split.
- **Push** (fanout worker actively delivering) is required for latency: a message should appear in under a second. Cost: work proportional to *connected* devices.
- **Pull** (device fetches from the log on reconnect) is required for correctness and scale: it works for offline users, for 1,024-member groups where 99.9% of devices are idle, and for multi-device bootstrap where pushing history to a new phone would be absurd.

The design that works: **push for the tip, pull for the backlog.** The fanout worker only pushes messages while `seq` is within the live window (say the last 200 seqs); anything older is left for pull. So a 1,024-member group costs 1,024 session lookups for recent messages and 1,024 *no-ops* for old ones, and a newly connected device does one ordered range scan. Broadcast channels (100k+ members) go **pull-only** with push reduced to a "you have N new messages" notification, because fanout to 100k members per message is not a real-time problem you solve by being clever.

Push-notification collapsing: if a user is offline and 50 messages arrive, send **one** push saying "50 new messages", not 50 pushes. This is both a cost and a UX decision, and it also reduces the temptation to fanout.

**7.3 Offline sync: the algorithm that actually matters**

On reconnect the client sends `(conversationId, lastSeq)` for each conversation it knows. Options:
1. **Per-conversation pull** — 200 conversations = 200 queries. Too many round trips.
2. **Batch by user**: a `sync_state` per user with `last_change_seq` per conversation, so the client sends one request listing all conversations with `lastSeq` and the server returns only those with new messages. **This is what production systems do.** The server computes the diff server-side from `conversation_inbox(user_id, conversation_id, last_seq, max_seq)`, which is a cheap table.
3. **Full resync** — only for a genuinely new device, and even then chunked so a 4-year-old history arrives in 50k-message pages in the background, with the user seeing recent messages immediately.

Add a **tombstone/`truncatedAtSeq`** field so that if history has been compacted away, the client learns the boundary explicitly instead of assuming a gap is a bug. Silent truncation is how chat clients get permanently corrupt state.

Delta-compression: a client that reconnects 5 minutes later and pulls 400 messages that are mostly a colleague's typing indicators would be wasteful, so receipts and typing events live on a separate, short-retention stream and are never part of history sync. Separate the *conversation* stream from the *ephemeral* stream.

**7.4 End-to-end encryption**

Design, not just "use TLS."
- **X3DH** (or its Signal successor) for asynchronous key establishment: a device publishes a signed prekey bundle (identity key, signed prekey, one-time prekeys). The sender fetches the bundle and performs a one-pass Diffie-Hellman triple, deriving a shared secret. This is what makes sending to an *offline* user possible — you encrypt before they come online.
- **Double Ratchet** per message: a symmetric ratchet (chain keys per message) plus a Diffie-Hellman ratchet on key rotation, giving forward secrecy (a compromised server key does not expose history) and break-in recovery.
- **Sender Keys** for groups: ratchet once per (group, sender) and derive each message key with a counter, so sending to 1,024 members is one chain, not 1,024 DH operations. Without Sender Keys, group E2EE is computationally hopeless.
- **Keys in the log**: store `ciphertext`, `iv`, `senderKey_id`. The server can enforce *policy* (one message per device, ratchet progression, epoch resets after adding a device) but never content. It can also do server-side *reporting* only with a separate user-consented reporting key.
- The cost, stated honestly: **multi-device E2EE requires the server to see a device's ciphertexts**, which is why multi-device became possible only after Signal's device-transport design. That means "E2EE + seamless multi-device" is a real engineering conflict and the resolution (device keys + encrypted device-to-device fanout) is worth explaining.
- Metadata is the actual leak: the server learns who talks to whom, when, and how often. Mitigations are sealed-sender / private contact discovery / padding / batched forwarding. Saying "metadata is the real attack surface" earns points.

**7.5 Persistent connections at 150M sockets**

Per-connection costs: file descriptor (1 per socket), memory (~4–10 KB of kernel + process buffers), and a TCP/TLS handshake at reconnect. Design consequences:
- **Long-lived WebSocket, not polling.** Polling at 3s intervals across 150M devices is 50k RPS of mostly-empty requests and still has 3 s latency. Polling only as a fallback for locked phones.
- **Stateless gateways**: all session state is in Redis (`presence:conn:{deviceId} -> gatewayId`). A gateway crash loses sockets but not state, and reconnect lands wherever.
- **Anycast / GeoDNS** so the client reconnects to a nearby gateway.
- **Slow rebalance**: 100k connections per node and ~60 handshakes/sec means a 100k-connection rebalance takes ~28 minutes. Plan failover as a multi-minute ramp with admission control, not an instant cutover. Offer this as the key operational insight.
- **Idle cost control**: app-level ping every 60 s, batched across all connections on a gateway (one timer, thousands of pings), with the ping doubling as a liveness signal. Transport keepalives at 30 s on 150M sockets is real money and real battery.
- **Push-only mode** when the app is killed: no socket, push wakes it. This is the majority of the day for most users, which is why the push path must be excellent.

**7.6 Abuse, flood control, and spam in chat**

Chat is the most abused surface on the internet; ignoring it is a design failure.
- Per-user send rate (token bucket), per-conversation rate, per-IP rate on account creation — reuse the limiter from §12.5.3.
- **Recipient-side blocking must be checked on the server**, since with E2EE the server cannot inspect content; it can enforce policy (no message to a blocked sender, no adding someone who blocks you).
- New-account rate limits, phone/email verification, and a trust score.
- Report flow: because the server cannot read the message, "report" must send *the client-held plaintext* (and optionally the associated key material) to a moderation service, only with user consent. This is a genuine E2EE tension and raising it unprompted is a strong signal.
- Group-size and message-rate caps; automated "you are sending unusually fast" friction rather than a hard block, because hard blocks get bypassed by creating a new account.
- Link and phone-number extraction happens on the **client** (it has the plaintext) and the report is submitted on user action.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

What breaks first
1. **The gateway fleet**, not the database. 1500 nodes is a large fleet to deploy, patch, and drain; connection churn during deploys is the single biggest source of user-visible "message not delivered" reports. Fix: connection draining with 0-RTT reconnect and versioned gateways, blue/green per AZ.
2. **Hot conversations.** A 1,024-member group or a broadcast channel is a single hot partition on `conversation_id`. The append is sequential (unavoidable), but fanout is not — shard fanout by `seq % k` for very large conversations, and use pull-only for broadcast.
3. **Log storage cost and retention.** 18 TB/day × 90 days hot = 1.6 PB on SSD. Tiering is mandatory and must be designed on day one, because retrofitting a retention policy over a live message log means a migration.
4. **Sync query cost for old conversations.** Scanning a 4-year-old conversation without the hierarchical index is a multi-minute query. This is the failure users complain about most ("I can't load old messages").
5. **Presence accuracy vs load.** Presence writes at 150M × (1/60) = 2.5M/sec. Needs Redis with aggressive TTLs and pipelining, or it becomes a permanent 30% of fleet CPU.
6. **Push notification volume.** Collapsing is not optional; un-collapsed push at 60B messages/day is both a cost and an uninstall driver.
7. **Spam waves** from one account fan out to thousands of conversations before detection. Needs a kill switch that operates at the fanout consumer, not just the ingress.
8. **Metadata/E2EE tension** — moderation cannot inspect content, so moderation latency grows and depends on user reports.

Scale thresholds
- < 1M users: one or two gateways, Postgres + Redis, pull-only delivery with push for offline. Do not build a fanout tier.
- 1M–50M: dedicated fanout workers, Kafka, hierarchical index, media transcoding.
- 50M+: multi-region with region-affinity routing, 1500-node gateway fleet, broadcast channels, full E2EE with device transport, tiered storage.
- Multi-region correctness note: route by conversation home region to preserve ordering, and accept cross-region read latency for the other regions' conversations. Do not attempt multi-writer per conversation.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Effect | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gateway node crash | its 100k sockets drop | clients reconnect via anycast; messages continue to be appended | clients retry with `clientMsgId`, dedupe makes it idempotent; ramp is gradual (~28 min for full drain) |
| Message log shard down | cannot append | **reject sends for that conversation shard** (503 + Retry-After) — never ACK without durability | replica promotion |
| Kafka down | append still works and is ACKed; fanout stalls | online delivery delayed; users still see messages on next pull | drain from Kafka; messages are safe because the log is the source of truth |
| Fanout consumer lag | latency grows | pull-sync covers it; switch to pull-only if lag > 30 s | scale consumers |
| Redis (session directory) down | cannot route delivery | push delivery stops; **pull sync still works** because it needs only the log | Redis failover; this is why pull is the safety net |
| Presence store down | presence wrong | report all users offline; no functional impact | self-healing |
| Push provider down | no wake-ups | users see messages on next app open | retry with backoff; dead tokens pruned |
| Object storage down | media fails | text messages unaffected; media upload fails with a clear error | multi-region bucket |
| Region loss | 33% of sockets and all routing for that region | region-local drain; messages for unaffected conversations continue; acknowledged messages preserved by cross-region log replication | DNS failover + gradual reconnection |
| Slow consumer on one device | its queue grows | drop to pull-only for that device and send a "you have N messages" hint | device catches up on reconnect |
| Single abusive account | flood | rate limit + recipient-side blocks; emergency kill switch at the fanout consumer | post-hoc ban |
| Ciphertext/key mismatch (device re-added) | messages undecryptable | show "messages unavailable" rather than corrupting history | key epoch reset, rekey |

The key architectural property: **the append-only log is the only durable dependency; everything else (fanout, push, presence, delivery) is an optimization that can be rebuilt from the log.** If you draw one arrow on the whiteboard, it is client → log → ACK. Everything else is downstream and disposable.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Delivery | push for the tip + pull for the backlog | pure push | pure push cannot serve offline users or multi-device bootstrap |
| Ordering | server-assigned per-conversation `seq` | client timestamps | mobile clocks are wrong; ordering must not depend on the device |
| Deletes/edits | tombstones in the log | in-place mutation | the append path never contends with reads |
| Unread count | `last_read_seq` per device | query the log | unread by scan is O(history) on every app open |
| Pagination | hierarchical index + keyset on `seq` | LIMIT/OFFSET | OFFSET is O(n) and impossible at this data volume |
| Presence | Redis TTL, lossy | strongly consistent presence | presence is worthless if it blocks; nobody needs a linearizable green dot |
| Connection | long-lived WebSocket + push fallback | short polling | latency and fleet cost |
| Encryption | X3DH + Double Ratchet + Sender Keys | TLS only | E2EE is a product requirement, not a transport one |
| Media | direct-to-object-storage presigned upload | proxy through the chat service | 100 MB through a chat gateway is absurd |
| Broadcast | pull-only | fanout to all members | fanout to 100k members per message is not solvable in real time |
| Multi-region | conversation home region, single writer | multi-writer per conversation | ordering is worth more than write locality |
| Mutex/lock for delivery | none | distributed lock per conversation | ordering comes from the log; locks add a failure mode for nothing |
| Delivery guarantee | at-least-once + client dedupe on `clientMsgId` | exactly-once | exactly-once does not exist across a mobile network; idempotency keys are the honest mechanism |

Rejected wholesale
- **Google-style RPC/SOAP with request-response only.** Chat is push; a client that must poll is a latency and battery disaster.
- **Single global message table with a global sequence.** One hot partition, no horizontal write scaling, and every region writes to one place.
- **Kafka as the message store.** Kafka is not a queryable history; pagination, per-conversation reads, and retention tiers all want a store. Kafka belongs in the fanout path, not the truth path.
- **Cassandra with `ORDER BY` for history.** Partition-key design for a conversation gives you exactly the append pattern you want; use it with a hierarchical secondary index for deep pagination, and don't pretend the DB handles fanout.
- **Storing plaintext with server-side encryption at rest.** Called "E2EE" by everyone, actually zero-knowledge to nobody. If the requirement is E2EE, the server must not be able to read it, full stop.
- **Per-message DB writes plus per-recipient DB writes.** Doubles the write amplification and couples the hot path to the recipient count.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "How does offline sync actually work when a phone has been off for a week?"**
Model answer: Nothing is pushed to a phone that is off, so the phone pulls. On app launch the client has a local SQLite replica and its `last_seq` per conversation, and it sends one batched request — not 200 per-conversation calls — listing `(conversationId, lastSeq)` pairs. The server diffs that against `conversation_inbox(user_id, conversation_id, last_seq, max_seq)`, returns only conversations with `max_seq > lastSeq`, ordered by recent activity. For each, the client requests pages of 50 messages with keyset pagination on `seq`, starting from its `lastSeq` going forward. Deep history for a conversation with a large gap uses the hierarchical index to jump directly to byte offsets instead of scanning. Recent messages render immediately and older pages load as the user scrolls. Two details matter: the response includes `truncatedAtSeq` when the beginning of history has been compacted away, so the client knows to render "start of available history" rather than treating the gap as corruption; and receipts plus typing indicators are on a separate short-retention stream so a week of them never enters the history pull. For a genuinely new device, I bootstrap the last ~500 messages first so the app is usable in one round trip, then backfill the rest in the background.

**Q2. "Why is fanout a separate service and not just push from the API server?"**
Model answer: Three reasons, all of them throughput or failure-isolation. First, scale: one message to a 1,024-member group is 1,024 delivery attempts, and doing that in the send request path means the sender's latency is coupled to the size of the group and to the health of 1,024 gateways. Decoupling means the send ACK waits only for the log append. Second, ordering and backpressure: Kafka keyed by `conversation_id` preserves per-conversation order and absorbs bursts — a viral group gets a backlog the consumers drain at their own rate, rather than a pile of timeouts in the API server. Third, failure isolation: if the fanout tier is down, the log keeps accepting writes and delivery happens later from Kafka; if fanout were inline, a fanout outage would be a send outage. The one thing that argues for doing it inline is simplicity, which is why small deployments should just do pull-only and skip the whole tier. And for broadcast channels the answer changes again: I go pull-only with a single notification, because per-message fanout to 100k members is not a real-time problem you can solve with better engineering.

**Q3. "How does E2EE work for a message to someone who is offline?"**
Model answer: Asymmetric key establishment, which is exactly what the Signal protocol's X3DH is for. Each device publishes a bundle: a long-term identity key, a signed prekey, and a batch of one-time prekeys, uploaded while online and replenished periodically. The sender fetches the recipient's bundle, performs the DH triple to derive a shared root secret, encrypts the message with a ratcheted chain key, and stores the ciphertext — which is all the server ever sees. The recipient's device performs the same computation when it comes back online, and decrypts. The one-time prekeys give forward secrecy for that batch; when the batch is exhausted the client uploads more while online. For groups, Sender Keys: the sender ratchets once per group and derives each message key from a counter, so 1,024 recipients cost one ratchet rather than 1,024 DHs. The honest cost is multi-device: to make a second phone work, the server must be able to fan out ciphertexts to it, which means device-to-device encrypted forwarding and a visible list of linked devices. That is a real weakening of the "only I hold the keys" story and it is why every messenger has a "linked devices" screen. Also worth stating: the server still sees the social graph, timestamps, and sizes, so sealed-sender and metadata padding matter more than most designs admit.

**Q4. "150 million sockets. Walk me through why your design survives a deploy."**
Model answer: Because deploys are the most common cause of message-delivery complaints, they get a specific design. Gateways hold **no durable state** — the session directory is in Redis, so I can kill a gateway and any client can reconnect to any other. That lets me do blue/green: bring up a new gateway fleet version, shift a fraction of the DNS/anycast weight or actively drain 5% of connections, watch the delivery-lag and reconnect-retry metrics, then ramp. Draining a node with 100k connections is the hard part, and it is slow by arithmetic: at roughly 60 handshakes/sec per node, rehoming 100k connections takes about 28 minutes, so a drain has to be a multi-minute ramp with a client-side reconnect budget, not a kill. On the client side I cap backoff and jitter so reconnect attempts are spread over minutes rather than becoming a thundering herd when a whole AZ goes away. The reason the log path makes this safe is `clientMsgId` idempotency: a client that reconnects mid-deploy and retries its send gets the same `seq` back, so the user sees one message, not two. And messages are never at risk because they were ACKed after the append, not after delivery.

**Q5. "What's the hot partition and what do you do about it?"**
Model answer: Any single conversation. All appends for a conversation must be totally ordered, which by definition puts them on one shard, so a group receiving 10 messages/sec is 10 sequential writes/sec on one shard — fine. The problem is fanout: that same 10 messages/sec to 1,024 members is 10,240 delivery attempts/sec, and to 100k broadcast members it is 1M/sec from one conversation. So I split the two concerns. The append stays strictly sequential — there is no way around that if you want ordering, and I am not going to pretend otherwise. The fanout goes to a Kafka partition keyed by `conversation_id` but consumed by a horizontally scalable worker pool, so the *work* parallelizes even though the *ordering input* is serial; the worker emits to N gateway batches, each of which is a tight loop of socket writes with no per-recipient service call. For very large conversations I shard the fanout itself by `seq % k`, which is safe because recipients do not require cross-message ordering *within the delivery attempt* — the client sorts by `seq` on receipt, and the log already guarantees order. For broadcast channels I abandon fanout entirely: pull-only plus one collapsed push. And if a single group is so hot that even the append saturates the shard, the real fix is admission control on the group plus honest conversation sharding for a "channel" modeled as many conversations, which is a product decision rather than an engineering one.

**Q6. "How do you handle spam and abuse without being able to read messages?"**
Model answer: Enforcement moves to metadata and to the client. Server-side I can enforce everything that doesn't require plaintext: per-user, per-conversation, and per-IP send rates using the same token-bucket limiter as the API, new-account friction (verified phone/email, trust score, sending-rate ramp), recipient-side blocks checked at send time — with E2EE the server cannot see whether a message is abusive, but it *can* enforce that you cannot message someone who blocked you, which kills the most common harassment path. I also add conversation-level caps and a hard global kill switch that operates at the fanout consumer, so one account flooding 10k conversations can be stopped mid-fanout rather than only at ingress. Client-side, because the client does hold the plaintext, I extract links and phone numbers, and the report flow uploads the decrypted message plus, on user consent, the associated key material to a moderation service — that consent step is the honest design, because a messenger that silently uploads plaintext "for safety" is not actually E2EE. Then I add friction rather than hard blocks for borderline behavior — "you've sent 300 messages in 10 minutes, are you sure?" — because hard blocks get circumvented with a new SIM in minutes, whereas sustained friction is genuinely expensive to evade. Finally, all of it is per-*recipient-value* rather than per-account: the metric I actually watch is how many people block or report a given sender per hour, because that catches coordinated spam rings that stay under every per-account rate limit.

---

## 12.5.5 News feed

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design the feed for a news app. Users follow sources and topics; the feed is the home screen. It's the highest-traffic page in the app."

The score is in the fanout section. Everyone knows "fan out on write" and "fan out on read." Almost nobody can say *when each breaks*, give the crossover number, or explain what happens at a celebrity scale. Secondary signals: ranking as a separate service, cold start for new users, and the fact that a feed is a cache with a write path and a purge path.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Home feed: ranked, paginated, infinite scroll.
- F2. Follow/unfollow sources and topics; block/mute users.
- F3. Ranking: recency + engagement + source authority + personalization, with diversity and freshness constraints.
- F4. Explore/trending, "For You", search-driven feed.
- F5. Dedupe, story grouping, "read" state.
- F6. Real-time-ish insert for very fresh items.
- F7. Moderation state (an item removed post-publication must leave the feed).

Non-functional
- N1. Feed load: **< 200 ms p99** from cache.
- N2. Rank time (inference + fetch): **< 100 ms p99**, separate from request serving.
- N3. Availability 99.9%+, with a **degraded but non-empty feed** as the goal — a home screen that shows "something went wrong" is a churn event.
- N4. Freshness: a breaking-news item visible within **60 s** of publication.
- N5. Post-publication unpublish/moderation must propagate in < 2 min.
- N6. Feed size: 20 items per page, cold start capped at 500 items then switch to "trending".

Non-goals: ads (separate pipeline), long-form article body delivery, comments.

---

**3. Estimation**

Scale assumption: 30M MAU, 6 sessions/day = 180M feed loads/day.
- 180e6 / 86,400 ≈ **2,083 loads/sec average**; peak 5× → **~10k RPS** at page-load level, or ~200k item-level deliveries/sec.

Fanout cost — the key calculation
- Assume 30M users, each follows 40 sources. Average followee publishes 5 items/day → total published items = **150M/day = 1,736 items/sec average**, 8,700/sec at peak.
- Fan-out on write (materialize): cost = publications × average follower count. If the distribution is power-law, average follower count across *publishers* is much lower than 40 — say 2,000 average → 1,736 × 2,000 = **3.47M feed writes/sec**. At 300 B per materialized entry that is **1 GB/s of writes, 86 TB/day**. Infeasible on Postgres; needs a wide KV at ~$0.10/GB-month = $8,800/month just for that tier, plus write CPU. Conclusion: fan-out-on-write is right only for high-fanout publishers.
- Fan-out on read (pull + rank + cache): cost = reads. 2,083 page loads/sec × 20 items = 41,660 item materializations/sec — **12× cheaper** than write fanout. Conclusion: read fanout wins for the long tail.
- Therefore the hybrid: **push for celebrities, pull for the long tail**, and state the crossover. The crossover is where `followers × publish_rate` exceeds the cache-fill rate you can afford. With 200k item-deliveries/sec of capacity, push is worth it above roughly 200k followers per publisher, which is why every real system has a "big accounts" list.

Storage
- Materialized feed entry: `user_id(16) + item_id(16) + score(4) + inserted_at(4)` ≈ 60–80 B with overhead.
- Push fanned out to 500M user feeds × 100 B = **50 GB** — comfortably in Redis/memcached with 3 replicas.
- Item store: 150M/day × 2 KB (title + thumbnail ref + body ref) = **300 GB/day**, 4.5 TB/90 days — this is the bigger storage number and people miss it.

Bandwidth
- 2,083 req/s × 60 KB (20 items with images + video autoplay previews) = **125 MB/s** ≈ 1 Gbps. Images must be CDN-served; the feed response carries URLs, not bytes.

Availability
- 99.9% = 43.8 min/month. Budget the degradation ladder: stale-but-valid feed > re-ranked-without-personalization feed > empty feed.

Arithmetic worth stating
- "3.47M writes/sec versus 42k reads/sec is an 82× difference. That single ratio is the whole architecture: do not materialize what you can compute on read."

---

**4. API design**

`GET /v1/feed?cursor=<opaque>&limit=20&surface=home|following|explore|foryou`
```json
{
  "items": [
    { "itemId": "i_8f3c", "type": "article", "source": { "id": "s_1", "name": "..." },
      "title": "...", "thumbnail": "https://cdn.../i_8f3c.jpg",
      "publishedAt": 1767000000, "score": 0.812, "readAt": null }
  ],
  "nextCursor": "eyJzIjo0MjExfQ",
  "generatedAt": 1767000042,
  "stale": false,
  "degraded": false,
  "reason": null
}
```
Cursor must be opaque and carry `(publishedAt, itemId)` plus the score, so that a re-rank between pages does not produce duplicates or skips.

`POST /v1/follows` / `DELETE /v1/follows/{sourceId}` → 202. Follows must be **eventually consistent** with the feed; a user must not wait for a re-rank to see a new follow.

`POST /v1/items/{itemId}/read` → 202, batched and sampled.
`GET /v1/feed/items?ids=...` → hydration of cached IDs in one call.
`POST /v1/feedback` → `{itemId, signal: view|like|share|hide|not_interested, dwellMs}` → 202.
`GET /v1/explore/trending?window=1h` → separate, cached service.

Error/degradation cases
| Case | Code | Behavior |
|---|---|---|
| Ranker timeout | 200 | return recency-ranked feed, `degraded=true, reason="ranker"` |
| Ranker down | 200 | recency-only, log for later training; **never 500 a home screen** |
| Hydration item missing (deleted) | 200 | drop from page, fill to keep page size, do not return a hole |
| Unfollowed source still in cached feed | 200 | filter client-side of the API by the follow snapshot; purge asynchronously |
| Blocked author | 200 | filter at hydration — must be at hydration, not at fanout, because blocks are per-reader |
| Cursor expired (> 7 days) | 200 | return `nextCursor: null` and `restart: true` |
| Feed cold (new user) | 200 | trending items, `stale=false`, `reason="cold_start"` |
| Over-fetch from a heavily followed source | 200 | per-source cap in a page, e.g. max 5 items per source per page |

That last row (per-source cap) plus a diversity constraint is the difference between a feed and a firehose.

---

**5. Data model**

```
items
  item_id          UUID PRIMARY KEY
  source_id        UUID
  author_id        UUID
  type             ENUM(article, video, short, live)
  title            TEXT(300)
  body_ref         TEXT        -- S3 key, never inline body
  media_refs       JSONB
  published_at     TIMESTAMPTZ
  status           ENUM(draft, published, removed)   -- removal for moderation
  authority_score  FLOAT       -- source-level, precomputed
  engagement       JSONB       -- rolling counts
```

```
follows
  follower_id, followee_id, created_at
  PRIMARY KEY (follower_id, followee_id)
  INDEX (followee_id)          -- fanout direction, needed for push fanout
```

Materialized feed (only for big accounts) — Redis or DynamoDB, sharded by user
```
feed:{userId}
  ZSET  member=itemId  score=rankScore       -- score = f(authority, engagement, freshness)
  TTL  7 days
  capped at 1,000 entries, trimmed by score
```
Key insight: score is a *rank score* with a time-decay term baked into it, which is why a ZSET works — Redis does not sort by a computed function, so the decay must be in the score or the trim must happen on a timer.

Ranking features table (feast-like)
```
item_features      item_id, source_id, age_s, ctr_1h, ctr_24h, velocity, topic_vec_ref
user_features      user_id, followed_source_set, topic_affinity, blocked_set, session_context
```

Interactions (append-only, columnar)
```
events(user_id, item_id, event, ts, dwell_ms, surface)  -- PARTITION BY day, TTL 30-90d
```

Moderation index (so removal can fan out)
```
removed_items   item_id, removed_at, blast_radius (follower count at removal)
```
Why removal is hard: a removed item may be materialized in millions of feeds. Fix is a kill-list consulted at hydration time (a Redis/bloom set of removed ids, hit on every feed read), plus a best-effort async purge from fanout caches. Consult-at-read is what makes propagation fast; async purge is what makes it durable.

Access patterns
| Pattern | Served by |
|---|---|
| Home feed read | feed cache → ranker → hydrator → item store |
| Read-after-follow | always pull that followee's recent items directly, for 1–2 hours, bypassing the cache |
| Trending | precomputed counters in Redis, 1-min buckets |
| Unpublish | kill-list lookup at hydration + async fanout purge |

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Client
  |
  +--> Feed Service (aggregator, thin)
  |     1. read candidate feed cache (feed:{userId} ZSET, or the pull set)
  |     2. miss -> Puller: fetch latest N per followee from Redis + recent-published list
  |     3. Ranker (separate service): features -> score -> top 20
  |     4. Hydrator: batch item lookup, filter blocks/removed/seen, attach media URLs
  |     5. write-back to feed cache with TTL
  |     6. response, with `degraded`/`stale` flags
  |
  +--> Ingestion Pipeline
        publish -> item store + "recent published" sorted set (partitioned by bucket)
        -> Kafka `item_published`
        -> Fanout Workers (ONLY for sources above the push threshold)
             -> write feed:{followerId} ZSET entries (async, retried, idempotent)
        -> Kill-list writer on removal
        -> Engagement aggregator (1-min rollups -> trending)
        -> Feature store writer
```

Read path, precisely
1. `GET /v1/feed`. Cache key `feed:v3:{userId}`. Hit → apply block filter, return (p99 ~20 ms).
2. Miss → Puller. It unions: the materialized ZSET for big accounts, plus for each of the user's 40 followees their latest items from the `recent:{sourceId}` sorted set (1 MGET), plus `recent:global` for anything in the last 5 minutes.
3. Filter: blocks, seen (> 7 days), removed via kill-list.
4. Ranker: fetch features, score, take top 50, apply per-source cap (max 5/page) and freshness floor.
5. Hydrator: batch-load items (single MGET), attach CDN URLs, strip items that failed moderation.
6. Write back the top 200 to the cache with 5-minute TTL (longer if the user is active, shorter if they are not — adaptive TTL).

Diagram: aggregator at the center with three fan-in sources (cache, puller, ranker), and the write pipeline off to the side.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Fan-out on write vs fan-out on read: the crossover, with numbers**

| Dimension | Fan-out on write (push) | Fan-out on read (pull) |
|---|---|---|
| Cost driver | publications × followers | reads × page size |
| Cost at our scale | 3.47M writes/sec, 86 TB/day | 42k materializations/sec |
| Read latency | best — item already there | one extra hop (still cached) |
| Write latency | **bad** — publisher waits for 2,000 shard writes | instant, publish is a single insert |
| Freshness | instant | up to TTL; needs a special path for very fresh items |
| Storage | 50 GB of per-user ZSETs, must be trimmed | tiny; only recent items per source |
| Failure mode | a failed fanout write silently drops an item | recomputation is automatic |
| Correctness | unfollow requires 2,000 deletes | filter at read, trivial |
| Works for | high-fanout sources (celebrities, big publishers) | long tail, new users, everything else |

Decision: **push above ~200k followers; pull below; hybrid always.** Plus a third mechanism for the freshness problem: items published in the last N minutes bypass the cache entirely and are merged into the response, computed from the `recent:global` sorted set. That fixes push's weakness (stale un-fanned-out items) and pull's weakness (missed fresh items) at once.

The failure mode nobody mentions: with fan-out on write, a partially-completed fanout means a user **silently misses** a post. With fan-out on read, a cache miss is self-healing. That asymmetry is why read fanout is the safer default and push is an optimization applied to a curated list of accounts.

**7.2 Ranking as a service, and the degradation ladder**

Ranker is separate because it is the most expensive thing in the request path and the most likely to be retrained and redeployed.

Layered scoring so that partial failure is graceful:
1. **Candidate generation** (retrieval): source-recency, topic similarity, trending, collaborative-filtering vector recall.
2. **Feature fetch**: user profile + item features from a low-latency store. Everything precomputed; **no feature computation on the request path**.
3. **Scoring**: a GBDT (fast, millisecond inference) first. A two-tower model for embeddings/retrieval, not for final ranking.
4. **Post-processing**: diversity (no more than 5 per source), freshness floor, no more than 3 ads-in-a-row, block/mute, already-seen filter.

Degradation ladder, in order: full ranker → recency + authority only (no personalization, ~5 ms) → global trending → top-curated list. All four return a usable feed. **Never return an error from the home screen.** Also add a `stale` flag so the client can show "pull to refresh" rather than silently serving stale content, and so the freshness SLO is measurable.

Ranking freshness: retrain daily, but retrain the *feature pipeline* hourly. A ranker scoring against 6-hour-old features is worse than a simple recency rank.

**7.3 Cold start, unfollow correctness, and "why did I see this?"

- **New user**: no follow graph, no history. Serve trending-in-geo + onboarding topic picks, and treat the first 20 sessions as an exploration phase with a higher exploration weight in the bandit.
- **Unfollow correctness**: the cache is stale by up to TTL, so a freshly unfollowed source can still appear. Two mechanisms: (a) filter at hydration using a follow-snapshot version passed along with the cache entry, and (b) an async purge that removes that source's items from the cached page for the affected users. (a) is cheap and prompt; (b) makes it durable.
- **"Why am I seeing this?"**: every served item carries the retrieval reason (`followed`, `similar_to`, `trending_in_region`, `friend_liked`). Storing the reason is a few bytes per item and it turns a support ticket into a one-line explanation. It is also the raw material for the "not interested" feedback signal.
- **Seen/dedupe**: a rolling per-user set of the last ~2,000 shown item ids, with item ids that are snowflake-like and therefore roughly time-ordered, so the set can be trimmed by age. Without it a hot source's repeat item dominates the feed.

**7.4 Removal, correction, and the kill-list**

Regulatory takedowns and moderation actions mean a published item must be able to leave the feed within ~2 minutes.
- **Authoritative source of truth**: `status='removed'` in the item store.
- **Kill-list**: an in-memory/bloom set of removed ids consulted at hydration on every read. Cheap (a few hundred thousand ids max) and it makes propagation as fast as the read path, not the write path.
- **Async fanout purge**: Kafka `item_removed` → for each affected user (tracked at fanout time in a `blast_map`), remove from the ZSET. Best effort, retries, dedupe.
- **Reads that race removal**: a client may already hold the payload. Handle at the edge with a short TTL on the item endpoint plus a 410 so cached copies expire quickly. Be honest that a determined client keeps a copy forever; the goal is server-side removal.
- **Blast-radius management**: store the follower count at publication so a takedown knows whether to run a targeted purge or just a kill-list. Items with 10M followers are a different operation from items with 40 followers.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

What breaks first
1. **Fan-out cost on a viral account.** A celebrity with 200M followers publishing one item = 200M ZSET writes. Fixes: threshold push (only push for a *rate-limited subset*), push only a "hot marker" and let readers pull the item, and an emergency switch to pull-only for that source. Design the kill switch before the incident.
2. **Cache hit rate.** If feed caches are small, every read becomes a pull+rank and p99 triples. Fix: adaptive TTL (5 min active, 30 s idle), capacity sized at ~20% of DAU × 2 KB.
3. **Hydrator fanout to the item store.** 200k item fetches/sec; a single Postgres will die. Fix: item metadata in a wide KV, images in a CDN, batch hydrations of 50–100 ids.
4. **Ranker latency spikes** on deploy or feature-store degradation. Fix: timeouts with graceful degradation, autoscaling on p99 not CPU, shadow mode before cutover.
5. **Trend computation** over the event stream. Fix: incremental counters in 1-min Redis buckets with a 5-min incremental top-K, never a full scan.
6. **Cache invalidation on removal** — see §7.4; without the kill-list, takedowns require touching millions of caches.
7. **Personalization data volume.** The feature store becomes the bottleneck; fix by materializing per-user features for active users only and expiring them.
8. **Stale trending due to bursty events**; fix with a decayed counter and separate windows.

Thresholds
- < 1M users: single service, pull + recency rank, Redis cache. Push fanout only if you have a genuinely big publisher.
- 1M–50M: separate ranker and hydrator, KV item store, push for sources > 200k followers, engagement rollups.
- 50M+: multi-region feed caches with region-local ranking, kill-list, full personalized ranker with vector recall, adaptive cache TTL.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Ranker down/timeout | recency + authority ranking, `degraded=true` | restart + shadow-compare outputs before re-enabling personalization |
| Feature store down | fall back to item-side features only | replica promotion |
| Feed cache down | every read does pull + rank; capacity must survive a 0% hit rate (design for 3×) | Redis failover, pre-warm |
| Item store down | cached metadata serves titles/thumbnails; no deep detail | fail the response partially rather than 500 |
| Hydrator timeout | return items with minimal metadata; the client fetches detail on tap | retry + alert |
| Puller slow | older pages only; top of feed fine | cache the pull set per user for 60 s |
| Kafka down | push fanout stops; pull covers everything except the freshest items | drain; items are in the item store regardless |
| Fanout worker crash mid-batch | some users miss the item | idempotent retry; plus a reconciliation job comparing publish time against fanout checkpoints |
| Removal event lost | item stays in feeds | kill-list polled from the item store periodically; reconciliation sweep |
| Celebrity publishes | cache misses spike; item store read storm | pre-warm, hot-item cache, push-threshold switch |
| Ad service down | serve organic only | budget reallocation |
| Bad personalization (model regression) | irrelevant feed, high hide rate | shadow deploy + a "reset personalization" flag; track hide-rate as an SLO |

The degradation ladder: **personalized ranked → recency+authority → trending → curated**. The invariant: the app always opens to something.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fanout | hybrid: push > 200k followers, pull below | pure push | 82× cheaper; push is where the hot publishers are |
| Unfollow | filter at hydration + async purge | delete from N feeds synchronously | deletes are unbounded work on a user action |
| Item removal | kill-list at read + async purge | update all feeds | same reason |
| Rank score in ZSET | time-decayed score | plain timestamp | ZSET sorts by stored score; decay must be materialized |
| Ranker | separate service, GBDT final rank | two-tower as final rank | GBDT is ms-inference on tabular features; embeddings are for recall |
| Feature computation | precomputed, stored | on-request computation | on-request features destroy the 200 ms budget |
| Cursor | opaque, carries seq + score | offset or bare timestamp | re-ranking between pages duplicates or skips items |
| Page diversity | per-source cap + freshness floor | pure top-K | without it the feed is a firehose |
| Freshness | merge last-N-minutes items on every read | rely on fanout only | fixes both push and pull weaknesses |
| Cache TTL | adaptive 30 s–5 min | fixed 10 min | idle users get stale; active users churn on reload |
| Read receipts | batched/sampled, stored | per-item write on read | 200k writes/sec for a signal nobody reads |

Rejected wholesale
- **Single materialized feed table per user in Postgres.** 500M rows-per-user sets of entries with ZSET semantics and a 7-day TTL — that is Redis/KV work, not relational work.
- **Compute the feed entirely at request time with no cache.** Beautiful, and it dies at 2k RPS. Always cache.
- **One global ranker across all users.** Removes personalization and creates a single hot pipeline whose output is wrong for everyone.
- **Fanout into a message queue per user.** You have just built a messaging system for read amplification; the log store is the wrong primitive.
- **ML on the request path.** Feature lookups, training, and inference all belong in precomputed stores and offline pipelines.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "When do you switch from fan-out on read to fan-out on write?"**
Model answer: When `publications × followers` for a source exceeds the read-fanout budget I can afford, which in our case is around 200k followers per source. The math: with 30M users and 40 follows each, read fanout costs reads × page size ≈ 42k materializations/sec, while write fanout for that same population costs publications × followers. If the average publisher had 2,000 followers, write fanout is 3.47M writes/sec — 82× more expensive — so it is only worth it where followers ≫ readers-per-publication. The practical trigger is not followers, though, it is the ratio of followers to how often people open the feed: a source with 1M followers where nobody opens the app does not need push. I maintain a dynamic threshold recomputed daily from the last 7 days of publish rate and read rate per source, and I keep a hard operational switch per source. Two traps: partial fan-out silently drops items, so pushes are idempotent with a reconciliation job; and a viral post from a 200M-follower account is 200M writes in seconds, so the switch has to be able to drop to pull-only mid-incident.

**Q2. "A user complains they see an article they already read, and one they unfollowed. How?"**
Model answer: Both are cache staleness, and both are fixed at hydration rather than at fanout. Already-read: I keep a rolling per-user seen-set of roughly the last 2,000 shown item ids; because item ids are snowflake-like and therefore roughly time-ordered, I can trim by age cheaply, and any item whose publish time is older than the seen window is treated as old content and ranked lower rather than dropped. The unfollow case: the cached page was materialized before the unfollow, so I carry a follow-snapshot version with the cached entry and, at hydration, filter against the user's current follow set; the follow service writes a `follow_changed` event that triggers an async purge of that source's items from the affected users' caches. I chose filter-at-read plus async purge over synchronous unfollow deletion because a synchronous version of this is unbounded work triggered by a user action — one user with 5,000 sources would enqueue millions of deletes. And I'd add "why am I seeing this?" on every item, storing the retrieval reason at ranking time, because both of these complaints become self-service the moment you can tell them the reason.

**Q3. "Your ranker is taking 800 ms. What do you do right now, at 3 a.m.?"**
Model answer: The ranker is wrapped in a 100 ms timeout with a fallback, so at 800 ms nothing breaks for users — they get the `degraded` path. First thing: check whether this is a deploy (a model version pushed 20 minutes ago) versus a feature-store degradation versus genuine load. If it's a deploy, roll back the ranker; the shadow-mode comparison I run before cutover exists exactly for this. The degraded path is recency + authority, which is roughly 5 ms and needs no feature store, so it is safe under any failure of the personalization stack. Then I look at whether any requests are actually timing out rather than just being slow — with a 100 ms timeout, 800 ms p99 could mean 7% timeout rate, which is a user-visible drop in feed quality. Two mitigations go in permanently: autoscale the ranker on p99 latency rather than CPU, since inference-bound services are latency-bound before they are CPU-bound; and cap candidate count adaptively, because the usual cause of a latency cliff is a deploy that raised candidate generation from 200 to 2,000. I would also verify the circuit breaker is on the read path and not only in the client, because a client that gets no ranker and no fallback will show an error screen, and that is the outcome I care most about avoiding.

**Q4. "How do you keep the feed fresh for breaking news?"**
Model answer: With a third mechanism that sits outside both fanout strategies. Anything published in the last N minutes — I use five — is merged into the response from a `recent:global` sorted set plus the `recent:{sourceId}` sets, bypassing the user feed cache entirely. That gives sub-minute visibility for everything, whether or not the fanout for that item has completed. On top of that I add a small "live tail" per active session: when a client is on the home screen, it holds a short-lived subscription that pushes at most one "new items available" signal per 15 seconds rather than items, so the client triggers a normal cached fetch. That avoids the failure mode where a websocket pushes 500 items into a mobile client during an election. The other half is correctness under fanout: publish writes to the item store and the recent set first, then emits to Kafka, so the item is always reachable by pull even if fanout is completely stalled. And for genuinely huge events I use the per-source switch to pull-only so the freshest path does not have to compete with a 200M-fanout write storm.

**Q5. "How would you add ads into this feed?"**
Model answer: Ads must be a separate pipeline with a separate contract, because an ad failure must never take down the organic feed. At request time the feed service reserves a small number of slots — no more than 10% of a page, and no more than two consecutive — and requests creatives from an ad server with a hard 50 ms timeout and a strict slot cap. On timeout or no-fill, the page simply has fewer items and is topped up from the organic candidates, so the response shape is unchanged. Targeting and auction happen outside the ranker; the ranker may influence which organic items get displaced via an explicit `adEligible` penalty, but ad *selection* never enters the ranking model, otherwise revenue starts silently optimizing for engagement-at-any-cost. Three things people miss: capping consecutive ads or users churn; needing an impression log at ad-serving volume (100k+/sec) routed to a separate billing pipeline with exactly-once semantics by ad impression id, because that is money and it must reconcile; and a kill switch per surface, so a bad campaign can be disabled without a deploy. Also mark served ads in the response so the client never renders an ad without the disclosure the compliance team needs.

**Q6. "How do you scale this to 500 million users?"**
Model answer: Three structural changes, not just more machines. First, region-local reads with global writes: feeds are read-mostly and users are geographically concentrated, so I run independent feed caches and rankers per region and replicate follows and item publications across regions asynchronously. The cost is that a new follow takes seconds to appear in another region, which is why read-after-follow bypasses the cache entirely for an hour — the user who just followed something must see it immediately, so that path reads from the follow graph directly and merges. Second, the cache has to be sized for 0% miss: at 500M users, 20% × 500M × 2 KB is 2 TB per replica, so a cache layer outage means a pull+rank storm that must survive at 3× normal read capacity. That forces the pull path to be genuinely fast and the item store to be a KV, not Postgres. Third, ranking becomes two-stage at that scale: vector recall for candidates and GBDT for final scoring, with the entire candidate generation done against precomputed per-user embeddings, so no request touches a training-serving-skew-prone feature join. Everything else — CDC of follows, Kafka for fanout and removals, the kill-list — is the same design, just at higher volume. And I would add cost as a first-class design constraint: at 500M DAU the feed cache is one of the largest line items in the bill, which is why adaptive TTL and the push/pull threshold are economics decisions as much as engineering ones.

---

## 12.5.6 Twitter timeline

⚫ Expert · **Deep dive**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design Twitter's home timeline. This is the hardest feed on the internet — celebrities, replies, retweets, algorithmic ranking, and hundreds of millions of users."

The interviewer is scoring one thing above all: do you know **why the hybrid timeline exists**, i.e. that pure fan-out-on-write is impossible for 500M followers and pure fan-out-on-read is impossible for 1B tweets/day, and therefore you split by *what the content is*, not by user. Second signal: do you know how replies and retweets break naive fanout. Third: do you treat ranking as a materialized, scheduled, non-request-time system.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Home timeline: algorithmic ("For You") and reverse-chronological ("Following").
- F2. Tweets, replies, quote tweets, retweets, likes.
- F3. Following graph, followers, blocking, muting, "hide this tweet", unfollow.
- F4. Real-time insert of new items into an open timeline.
- F5. Pagination backwards in time; "load new tweets" affordance.
- F6. Notification-worthy events (likes, replies, follows) — see §12.5.7.
- F7. Explore/trending topics.
- F8. Timeline consistency: items must not appear twice; ordering must not go backwards.

Non-functional
- N1. Timeline load **< 300 ms p99**, largely from cache.
- N2. 5M QPS at peak (Twitter-scale); for the exercise, ~50k RPS is a fair target.
- N3. Freshness of a followed account's tweet: **< 10 s**.
- N4. Ranking pipeline latency: **minutes, not milliseconds** — this is the defining property vs a news feed.
- N5. Removal (takedown/moderation) propagates **< 1 min**.
- N6. Availability 99.9%+ with a non-empty fallback at all times.
