- N7. Never re-rank the same item differently on pagination (deterministic enough to not duplicate).

Non-goals: search (Elasticsearch-based, separate), DMs, video transcoding.

---

**3. Estimation**

Write side
- 500M DAU × 200 tweets+actions/day → **1,158 tweets/sec average**; peak 4× → **~5k tweets/sec**. Reposts and quotes included: ~20k/sec peak.
- Total tweets/day ≈ 300M (including replies).

The fanout impossibility — the core calculation
- Average tweets/sec (5,000) × average follower count. With a power-law follower distribution, the *follower-weighted average* is dominated by a handful of accounts. Total (tweet, follower) pairs per second = total follower-follows × posting rate. Sum of followers across all active accounts ≈ **3 billion follow edges**. If 5,000 accounts tweet per second and the follower-weighted mean is 1,000,000, that is **5 billion feed writes/sec**. Absolutely impossible: at 300 B each, 1.5 TB/s.
- Read fanout is also impossible: 5M QPS × 20 items = **100M candidate materializations/sec**.
- Therefore: **hybrid by content class**, and the split is:

| Class | Example | Fanout | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Celebrity / power account | account with 100k–500M followers | **push**, on write, async, rate-limited | follower/readers ratio is extreme; precompute |
| Normal account | 100–100k followers | **pull on read**, from the follow graph | write fanout cheaper than read fanout |
| Everything | — | **both merged at read time**, then ranked | correctness safety net |

Push cost after splitting: assume 2,000 power accounts produce 500 tweets/sec total, weighted-mean 5M followers = **2.5B feed writes/sec** — still too much. So push is further throttled: rate-limited to ~1M entries/sec per follower-heavy cohort, with the remainder served by read-path pull for that account's items within the freshness window. The real system reduces this further by **not pushing to inactive users** and by batching.

Storage
- Push entry: `user_id(16) + tweet_id(16) + score(4) + ts(4)` ≈ 50 B.
- 1M tweets/day pushed at mean 5k followers = 5B entries/day. At 50 B = **250 GB/day**. Retained 3 days in a KV = **750 GB**; with 3 replicas ≈ 2.2 TB. That is a real, quotable number.
- Tweet store: 300M/day × 1 KB = **300 GB/day**, 27 TB for 90 days, in a KV sharded by `tweetId`.

Bandwidth
- 5M QPS × 50 KB/page = **250 GB/s** — the timeline is one of the largest content pipelines on the internet. Images are CDN; the API response carries URLs.
- Serving 50k RPS (exercise scale): 2.5 GB/s.

Availability
- 99.9% = 43.8 min/month, but the *product* requirement is stronger: Twitter's timeline is an ad surface, so a degraded timeline is a revenue event. Budget a graceful path that always returns items.

Arithmetic to state aloud
- "3 billion follow edges means the average account has ~6 followers' worth of work per tweet if I fan out on write. The problem is not the average, it is the tail: one tweet from a 300M-follower account is 300M writes, and at 300 B that is 90 GB for a single item. No amount of sharding helps; only not fanning out to everyone helps."

---

**4. API design**

`GET /2/timeline?surface=home|following|foryou|explore&cursor=<opaque>&count=20`
```json
{
  "data": [
    { "tweetId": "1815...", "authorId": "u_12", "text": "...", "createdAt": 1767000000,
      "entities": {...}, "mediaRefs": ["m_9"], "replyToId": null,
      "retweetedBy": null, "quotedTweetId": null,
      "rankReason": "in_network_boost", "conversationId": "c_441",
      "promoted": null }
  ],
  "nextCursor": "MTc2NzAwMDAwMDo=",
  "newItemsAvailable": 12,
  "algorithmVersion": "rank-v41",
  "degraded": false
}
```

`POST /2/tweets` → `201` with `{tweetId, createdAt}`. Must return fast and never block on fanout.
`POST /2/tweets/{id}/retweets` / `likes` / `replies`
`PUT /2/friendships/following` → 202. Follow/unfollow is eventually consistent; a new follow must appear within 10 s, so the read path checks the graph directly for recently-followed accounts.
`POST /2/hide` → `{tweetId}` → 202, deletes from the user's timeline immediately.
`DELETE /2/tweets/{id}` → tombstone + kill-list.
`GET /2/timeline/stream?surface=home` → WebSocket pushing **only** `{count: N}` "you have new tweets", not the tweets.

Error/degradation cases
| Case | Behavior |
|---|---|
| Ranker output missing for a page | fill from recency candidate set; `degraded=true` |
| Timeline cache miss storm | serve from pull path; throttle by session cookie |
| Hydration finds tweet deleted | drop and backfill from the candidate pool |
| Author now blocking the user | filter at hydration, per-reader |
| Duplicate across pages | cursor carries `(virtualTime, tweetId)`; merge by id |
| Out-of-order insert | score carries virtual time; never reorder below the cursor |
| Push fanout failed | pull path finds it within the freshness window |
| Kill-list hit | drop from response; mark session so client removes from DOM |

---

**5. Data model**

```
tweets
  tweet_id        BIGINT       -- snowflake, time-ordered
  author_id       BIGINT
  conversation_id BIGINT
  reply_to_id     BIGINT NULL
  quote_of_id     BIGINT NULL
  text            TEXT(280)
  media_refs      VARBINARY
  created_at_ms   BIGINT
  status          ENUM(active, deleted, protected)
  lang            TEXT(2)
  PRIMARY KEY (tweet_id)   -- sharded by tweet_id
  INDEX (author_id, tweet_id DESC)   -- user timeline
```

```
follow_graph
  follower_id, followee_id, created_at
  PRIMARY KEY (follower_id, followee_id)
  -- sharded by follower_id for "who do I follow"
  -- secondary index by followee_id for "who follows me" and fanout subscription
```
A single edge table with both access patterns needs two indexes or two tables; in practice the fanout worker maintains a separate **follower-list shard by followee_id** so fanout does not scan the primary index.

Candidate store (the read path's source of truth)
```
recent:{followeeId}      ZSET  score=virtualTime  member=tweetId   -- TTL 24h
outbox:celebrity         ZSET  score=virtualTime  member=tweetId   -- for the pull fallback
```

Pushed timeline
```
tl:{userId}              ZSET  score=rankScore  member=tweetId
  TTL 72h, capped at 2,000 entries
```
`rankScore` is precomputed by the batch ranker; the ZSET is the materialized output, which is why ranking does not need to be on the request path.

Ranking candidate sets (output of the batch job)
```
cand:{userId}           200 tweet ids   -- ANN retrieval, refreshed hourly
cand:social:{userId}   in-network boost list
cand:global:engage      global engagement leaderboard
cand:global:trends      trending topics
```

```
kill_set              removed/blocked ids, in Redis + bloom, checked at hydration
```
```
seen:{userId}         rolling last 5,000 shown tweet ids (tweet_id is time-ordered, so trim by age)
mute/block sets       per-user, in Redis, checked at hydration
```

Why `tweetId` must be snowflake-like: it gives (a) locality for the shard, (b) a free time ordering for trimming the `seen` set, (c) a stable tiebreaker for cursors, (d) id generation without a coordination service. Say all four.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Client
  |
  +--> Timeline Service (aggregator)
  |     1. read tl:{userId} (pushed, pre-ranked)  -- covers power accounts
  |     2. read recent:{followeeId} for normal followees not covered by push
  |        (graph fetched from graph store; cached 60s per user)
  |     3. MERGE candidates -> dedupe by tweetId -> filter blocks/mutes/kill_set/seen
  |     4. fetch ranking metadata (score from tl:, or recency for pulled items)
  |     5. hydrate (batch tweet lookup, media URLs, author + relationship status)
  |     6. backfill to keep page size; paginate by (virtualTime, tweetId)
  |
  +--> Write Pipeline
  |     POST /tweets -> tweet store -> outbox -> (async)
  |        +--> Popularity Counter (follower count, EWMA of tweet velocity)
  |        +--> if followers > THRESHOLD -> Push Fanout workers -> tl:{userId}
  |        +--> always -> recent:{authorId} (pull fallback, TTL 24h)
  |        +--> Kafka `tweet_created` -> trending agg, notifications, ads index
  |
  +--> Ranking Pipeline (HOURLY per active user, plus on-write deltas for power accounts)
  |     candidate gen (ANN + social + global) -> feature fetch -> GBDT + neural rank
  |     -> engagement prediction -> policy filters (ads, topics, quality)
  |     -> WRITE SCORES INTO tl:{userId}   (no work on the read path)
  |
  +--> Graph Service (followers/following, blocks, mutes)
  +--> Hydration Service (tweets + authors + relationship in one batched call)
```

Read path: cache → merge pushed + pulled candidates → filter → hydrate → paginate. The single most important line for the interviewer is that **ranking scores are written by an offline/hourly job, and the request path only sorts by stored score.**

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 The hybrid fanout split, and why the split is by content class**

Push side, math: total follow edges ≈ 3B. Twitter's real solution is to define the threshold in terms of the *read/write ratio* per publisher, not followers:
- Compute per account `W = tweets_per_day × followers`. If `W / (timeline_loads_per_day × 20)` is above ~1 (i.e. fanout costs more than the reads it would serve), push is cheaper.
- Practically this lands at ~100k followers. Accounts above it: ~2,000 accounts, producing maybe 500 tweets/sec, weighted mean 5M followers → 2.5B writes/sec. Still too much, so push is additionally:
  - **asynchronous** — never on the send request path,
  - **sharded by follower** so it parallelizes across ~500 workers,
  - **rate-limited per follower cohort**, and
  - **skipped for inactive users** (no push session in 7 days) — this alone removes a large fraction.
- Pull side: for a user with 800 followees, reading `recent:{id}` for each is 800 ZSET reads. That is why the **follow graph is cached per user for 60 s** and why candidate generation for followees is done as a batched MGET over a pipelined connection, or better, a single server-side `getRecentForGraph(userId)` call in the graph service that fans out internally. Per-request fanout to 800 shards is not viable from a request thread; it has to be a dedicated service with its own connection pools and timeouts, returning a bounded candidate count (e.g. top 3 per followee, 200 followees max, prioritized by engagement velocity).

Consequence to state: **there is no design where a 300M-follower account's tweet is materialized for everyone.** Push handles the top of the distribution and pull handles the rest, and for the very top accounts the pull fallback (the `outbox` sorted set) is the safety net that prevents a missed tweet.

**7.2 Replies and retweets: where the fanout model breaks**

Three concrete problems and three fixes:
1. **A reply to a big account.** If replies are pushed to the *parent author's* followers, a reply generates as much fanout as the original tweet — abuse vector, and it breaks the cost model. Fix: replies are **never push-fanned-out**. They live in the conversation thread, and reach people through (a) the conversation's participants, and (b) a much cheaper "thread affinity" candidate source that pulls in up to 20 replies per conversation for the timeline.
2. **A retweet by a mid-size account.** Naive fanout means the retweet goes to the retweeter's followers, who may not follow the author — this is correct behavior but it means the reach of a single tweet is unbounded, and it is the primary spam and misinformation vector. Fix: retweets are **not materialized into the timeline at all**. At read time, a retweet is resolved by joining the retweeter's timeline to the original tweet and rendering an attribution line. Cost per read is one lookup, and fanout is bounded by the retweeter's own activity, which is rate-limited and trust-scored.
3. **Conversations / threads.** A thread of 20 tweets is 20 items. Fix: **conversation-level ranking.** Score the thread once, cap the number of in-conversation items in a page (e.g. max 3), and let the rest be reachable by tapping through. This both fixes cost and dramatically improves the experience of reading a timeline.

The general principle: **rank and fan out at conversation granularity, render at tweet granularity.** This is the single most valuable sentence in this answer.

**7.3 Ranking as a materialized, scheduled system**

Twitter's timeline ranking is a batch job, not a request. Why, and what the pipeline looks like:

Why offline
- Candidate generation over 300M tweets/day cannot run per request (ANN search over a corpus that large is tens of ms at best, plus feature joins).
- A per-request ranker would put model inference, feature-store lookups, and policy filtering in the 300 ms budget — it has been tried and abandoned for latency and cost reasons.
- Freshness is handled by an **on-write delta path**: new items get a provisional score from the popularity counter and are inserted into `tl:{userId}` immediately, then re-scored by the next batch run. So the timeline is stale-by-hours in scoring but never stale in content.

Pipeline, hourly for active users
1. **Candidate generation**: ANN retrieval over tweet embeddings (recall 500–1,000), plus in-network boost (followees' recent high-velocity tweets), plus global engagement and trending leaders. Union, dedupe, cap at ~1,000.
2. **Feature fetch**: user features, tweet features, author features, contextual features. All from a low-latency store; **never computed at request time**.
3. **Ranking**: a GBDT or DLRM over those features. Multi-stage: a cheap first pass over 1,000 candidates, a neural reranker over the top 50, then an engagement-prediction model that estimates the probability the user will like, reply, or retweet it, which is folded into the score.
4. **Diversity and policy post-processing**: no more than N from one author, no more than M consecutive from the same topic, dedupe near-identical content, quality/demote classifiers, political-balance and legal policy filters, ad slots.
5. **Materialize**: write scores into `tl:{userId}`, TTL 72h, cap 2,000 entries. Skip users with no sessions in 7 days.
6. **Shadow/evaluate**: score a small holdout with the candidate model before promoting, and track hide-rate, mute-rate, and negative-feedback rate as first-class SLOs.

Trade-off to name honestly: hourly re-ranking is *worse* for relevance than a request-time ranker, and it is a deliberate exchange of ~1–5% relevance for an order of magnitude in cost and latency. Twitter is widely thought to have lost relevance to this; the honest engineering answer is that a hybrid exists — batch scores as the base, plus a lightweight request-time adjustment for **very fresh items** (last 30 minutes) using the on-write provisional score, so breaking news still gets a relevance boost.

**7.4 Cursor pagination in a mutating feed**

The problem: between page 1 and page 2, new items are inserted at the top, items are deleted, and scores are re-computed. Offset pagination duplicates and skips; time-only cursors tie.
- Cursor = `(virtualTime, tweetId)` where `virtualTime = score or created_at` — a monotonic, stable-enough key. Next page: `WHERE score < cursor.score OR (score = cursor.score AND tweetId < cursor.tweetId)`, merged across the pushed ZSET and every pulled `recent:` set.
- Because scores change between pages, exact stability is impossible. The practical mitigation is to **store the ranking version** in the cursor (`rank-v41`) and serve subsequent pages from a snapshot of that version where possible; otherwise accept that an item can shift, and make the client tolerant (dedupe by id, tolerate reordering).
- Deleted items: the kill-list removes them at hydration, and the page is backfilled so the page size stays constant — a hole in a timeline reads as a bug.
- The "N new tweets" affordance: a WebSocket or a poll sends only a count, and the client does a normal fetch with a fresh cursor. Never push the items themselves; that is how you end up with 400 items injected into a mobile client.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

What breaks first
1. **Celebrity fanout**, at write time, in seconds. One tweet from a 300M-follower account is 90 GB of writes. Fixes: threshold + async + skip inactive + the `outbox` pull fallback + an emergency per-account switch.
2. **Follow-graph fanout on read** for users with thousands of followees. Fixes: cap graph size used for pull (e.g. 200 followees by activity), batch MGET, dedicate a service with its own pools, and cover the rest by push.
3. **Ranking cost.** Hourly ranking of 300M DAU at 1,000 candidates each is 3 × 10¹¹ candidate scorings per day. At ~10 µs per GBDT inference that is 3M core-seconds/day ≈ 35 vCPUs, plus feature-store reads which dominate. Fixes: rank only active users, shrink the candidate set, TTL the user model.
4. **Candidate generation recall/precision** — a quality wall, not a scaling wall, but it is what actually determines engagement.
5. **Timeline cache size**: 500M users × 2,000 entries × 50 B = 50 TB. Even at 10% active (50M) it is 5 TB per replica. This is the number that makes the push approach expensive and drives the "cap at 500 entries for inactive users" decision.
6. **Real-time insert** at scale: a burst of 200k tweets/minute inserting into millions of timelines. Fixed by async batching and by the fact that inserts are idempotent per (user, tweet).
7. **Spam and coordinated inauthentic behavior** — the reason fanout is rate-limited and trust-scored.
8. **Removal fanout** — a viral deleted tweet must disappear from millions of caches.

Thresholds
- < 5M users: pull-only with graph fanout, recency scoring, Redis cache. Do not build push.
- 5M–100M: push threshold at ~100k followers, hourly batch ranker, conversation-level ranking, kill-list.
- 100M+: on-write provisional scoring, ANN candidate retrieval, per-conversation caps, outbox pull fallback for top accounts, multi-region read with global async follow replication.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Push fanout down/stopped | power-account tweets missed by pushed timeline; **`recent:` pull still finds them** | drain Kafka; reconciliation sweep comparing published vs fanned-out |
| Graph service down | cannot compute pull candidates; serve pushed timeline only | replica; timeline still has power accounts |
| Timeline cache down | full pull+rank on every request | must survive 0% hit at 3× capacity; KV replica promotion |
| Ranker job fails | scores go stale; provisional on-write scores remain | rerun; alert on score freshness (a real SLO) |
| Hydration timeout | minimal metadata; client fetches detail | retry |
| Kill-list propagation fails | removed tweet stays in timelines | reconciliation + direct purge for viral removals |
| Trending aggregator down | explore degrades to recency | recompute from events |
| Starvation (a huge account pushing) | small accounts' fanout queues lag | **per-account rate limits and per-cohort fair queueing** so one account cannot starve the system |
| Notification/engagement storm | fanout worker saturation | decouple via Kafka; notifications never share a worker pool with timeline fanout |
| Region loss | timeline served from another region; follow replication lag means new follows missing | region failover; read-after-follow bypasses cache |
| Bad ranking model | irrelevant timeline, hide-rate spike | shadow deploy + revert flag; hide-rate as an SLO with an alert |

The invariant: **the timeline is never empty and never errored.** Every failure path ends in recency from `recent:` sets, and if that fails, a globally trending list. And the push/pull redundancy is what makes the fanout tier's outage invisible.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fanout | hybrid, threshold at ~100k followers | pure push (3B writes/sec) | arithmetically impossible |
| | | pure pull at 5M QPS | 100M candidate materializations/sec |
| Ranking | hourly batch, scores materialized into the ZSET | request-time ranker | 300 ms budget and cost; batch trades ~1–5% relevance for 10× |
| Freshness | on-write provisional score + "N new" signal | waiting for the batch | breaking news must surface in seconds |
| Replies | never push-fanned-out | inherit the parent's fanout | replies would explode the cost model and enable spam |
| Retweets | resolved at read, not materialized | fan out the retweet | bounds reach and cost; primary abuse vector |
| Ranking granularity | conversation-level | per-tweet | cost and readability; cap in-conversation items per page |
| In-network boost | explicit social graph feature | pure engagement ranking | engagement alone collapses to outrage and hurts long-term retention |
| Timeline cache | adaptive TTL + 500-entry cap for inactive users | store 2,000 for everyone | 5–50 TB; cost is a design constraint |
| Pagination | `(score, tweetId)` cursor + ranking version | offset or time-only | no duplicates or skips |
| Real-time | push a *count*, let the client refetch | push the items | client memory/battery and payload size |
| Removal | kill-list at read + targeted purge | update every timeline | unbounded work |
| Ad integration | separate ad server, reserved slots, timeout | ads in the ranker | ads must never degrade the organic path |

Rejected wholesale
- **One global ranking pipeline for all users.** Removes personalization and creates a single hot job whose output is wrong for everyone.
- **Storing full tweet content in the timeline cache.** 200× the bytes for the same ranking behavior; store ids and scores, hydrate on read.
- **Full-graph fanout on read with no cap.** A user with 5,000 followees turns a 300 ms budget into 5,000 round trips.
- **Strong consistency on follows.** Blocking a user must take effect at read time (that is a read-path filter, which is instant), but unfollowing does not require fanout-wide consistency; paying for it here is wasted money.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "How do you make sure a 300M-follower account's tweet appears for everyone?"**
Model answer: I do not, and that is the design. Push-fanning-out a 300M-follower tweet is 90 GB of writes for one item, so above the push threshold I fan out in waves: an immediate wave to active sessions (recent sessions, say 7 days), and a wave to everyone else that is paced and rate-limited by follower cohort. The correctness net is the pull path — every tweet, regardless of fanout, is written to `recent:{authorId}` with a 24-hour TTL, so the timeline reader can always union in the last 24 hours of a followee's tweets. That means a tweet is visible to a cold user within 24 hours by pull, and to everyone else in seconds by push. There is also an emergency per-account switch that moves an account to pull-only during a spike, which is what you use when the 300M write storm is in progress. And I reconcile: a job compares `published_at` against fanout checkpoints so an account that was partially fanned out is detected and topped up. The honest version of the answer is that "appears for everyone" is eventually true within 24 hours by pull, seconds by push for active users, and there is no mechanism that makes it instant for all 300M, because the arithmetic does not permit one.

**Q2. "Why batch ranking instead of ranking at request time?"**
Model answer: Three reasons, in order of weight. Latency: request-time ranking means candidate generation over hundreds of millions of tweets plus feature joins plus inference inside a 300 ms budget, which historically landed at 1–2 seconds, and no amount of optimization gets ANN search over that corpus to a few milliseconds. Cost: hourly ranking of 300M daily actives at 1,000 candidates each is 3 × 10¹¹ candidate scorings per day, about 35 vCPUs of pure GBDT inference and far more in feature-store reads; doing that per request would be 10–20× the read volume of the entire service. And stability: a batch score is materialized into the ZSET, so the read path is a `ZRANGE` plus a hydrate — about 20 ms — and its p99 is decoupled from every downstream dependency. The cost is real and I will name it: scores are up to an hour stale, which measurably hurts relevance for fast-moving interests. That is why there is an on-write provisional score from a velocity counter, so new items get a reasonable score immediately, and why the best version of this design is hybrid — batch for the bulk, plus a lightweight request-time adjustment for the last 30 minutes of items. If you told me the read budget were 2 seconds and the corpus 10× smaller, I would rank at request time and skip the batch entirely.

**Q3. "How do replies and retweets affect fanout, and what did you do about it?"**
Model answer: Both break the cost model, differently. A reply inherits the parent's audience, so if you push replies you give anyone the ability to fan out to 300M people by replying to a celebrity — that is both a cost disaster and a spam catastrophe. Replies are therefore never push-fanned-out; they reach the timeline through the conversation's participants plus a bounded "thread affinity" source that adds at most ~20 replies per conversation per read. Retweets are different: they do need to reach the retweeter's followers, who often do not follow the author, but materializing them means one original tweet's reach becomes unbounded. So retweets are not materialized either — at read time the retweeter's timeline is joined to the original tweet and rendered with attribution, which costs one lookup per read and bounds the blast radius to the retweeter's own activity, which is rate-limited and trust-scored. The bigger lesson from both is to change the unit: **rank and fan out at conversation granularity, render at tweet granularity.** Score a 20-post thread once, cap in-conversation items at ~3 per page, and let the reader tap through. That single change fixes the cost, fixes the readability, and removes the reply-spam vector.

**Q4. "Your timeline cache is 5 TB. What's in it and how do you keep it fast?"**
Model answer: It is ids and scores, never content: `tl:{userId}` as a ZSET of tweet ids scored by the batch ranker, 50 B per entry. Sizing: 500M users at 2,000 entries is 50 TB, which is untenable, so entries are capped at 2,000 for active users and ~500 for anyone with no session in 7 days, which brings the realistic working set to 5 TB per replica. Speed comes from four things. TTL discipline: 72 hours, so it self-cleans. Adaptive write-back: a user's first page is cached for 5 minutes if they are active and 30 seconds if they are not, because caching a cold user's page for 5 minutes is pure waste. Sharding by user so a hot celebrity's writes do not concentrate. And — the important one — the read path does not hit the cache for its full candidate set; it hits the cache for the top of the timeline and relies on the merge with the pull path only for the tail. That way the cache holds a page's worth of scores plus some headroom rather than a full history. If the cache is unavailable, the design must survive 0% hit rate at 3× normal capacity, which is a capacity-planning requirement I would set explicitly rather than discover.

**Q5. "How do you handle the case where a viral tweet is deleted for legal reasons?"**
Model answer: Removal has to be fast and it has to be auditable. First, the authoritative change is `status='deleted'` in the tweet store plus a tombstone row, and that write goes to a kill-list in Redis and a bloom filter. The kill-list is checked at hydration on **every** timeline read, which means propagation is bounded by the read latency — a few hundred milliseconds — rather than by the speed of any fanout purge. That is the key mechanism; everything else is cleanup. Second, an async purge fans out over Kafka to remove the id from `tl:{userId}` for users who received it, using a blast map captured at publish time so you know who to target; it is best-effort with retries because the kill-list already covers correctness. Third, the CDN entry for media is purged immediately and the media endpoint starts returning 410 so cached copies expire quickly. Fourth, the removal event goes to an audit log with actor, legal basis, timestamp, and scope, because a regulatory takedown without an auditable trail is its own problem. And I am honest in the response: any client that already downloaded the bytes still has them. Server-side removal is the achievable guarantee; making the bytes unrecoverable is not, and pretending otherwise is worse than saying it.

**Q6. "How would you scale this to 500M DAU, and what breaks first?"**
Model answer: Four structural changes, and the first thing to break is celebrity fanout, not throughput. Push is capped by a per-account rate limit and by a fair-queue across follower cohorts so one account cannot starve the fanout workers; everything above the push threshold is carried by the `recent:{authorId}` pull path, which is why that sorted set is the real safety net rather than an optimization. Second, the follow-graph read fanout becomes its own service with dedicated connection pools and a hard cap on followees traversed per read, prioritized by engagement velocity — I cannot fan out to 5,000 followees inside a 300 ms request, so I take the top ~200 and let push cover the rest. Third, ranking is hourly over active users only, with the candidate set produced by ANN retrieval over tweet embeddings plus in-network and global leaders, and all scores written into `tl:{userId}`; the read path then only sorts by stored score. Fourth, the timeline cache is sized deliberately — 500 entries for inactive users, 2,000 for active — which is the difference between 5 TB and 50 TB, and that is a real bill. Everything else — Kafka, the kill-list, hydration — is the same design at higher volume. The one thing I would change at this scale is multi-region reads with asynchronously replicated follows, plus a read-after-follow path that bypasses the cache, because a user who just followed someone must see them within 10 seconds and cross-region replication lag is larger than that.

---

## 12.5.7 Notification system

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design a notification system. Anything can generate a notification — a comment, a like, a mention — and it has to reach tens of millions of users across email, push, SMS, and in-app, without sending anything twice."

The score is in: the fanout explosion ("one user with 5M followers likes something"), the many-channel delivery state machine, per-user per-channel preferences and quiet hours, exactly-once perception, and the fact that **the notification system must never take down the thing that generates it**.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Event sources: comments, replies, mentions, likes, follows, shares, mentions-in-thread, system notices.
- F2. Channels: in-app (live), push, email, SMS, digest.
- F3. Per-user, per-type, per-channel preferences, including opt-in requirements.
- F4. Quiet hours / Do Not Disturb with timezone handling and deferral.
- F5. Grouping/collapsing ("Lena and 3 others liked your post").
- F6. Digest batching (hourly/daily rollup).
- F7. Idempotent delivery — no duplicates per (user, event, channel).
- F8. Rate capping per user across channels to prevent notification spam.
- F9. Delivery receipts, retries, and a per-user notification inbox.

Non-functional
- N1. Generation: **< 50 ms added latency** to the triggering action. Never in the request path.
- N2. Fanout: 100k+ events/sec at peak; one event can fan out to 10M recipients.
- N3. In-app delivery (websocket) end-to-end **< 2 s p99**.
- N4. Email/ SMS within 60 s; digest on schedule.
- N5. Availability 99.9%; the notification system is **strictly lower priority than the primary action** and must degrade by dropping notifications, never by affecting the write.
- N6. Exactly-once *perception*: at-least-once delivery with idempotent rendering is acceptable and is what you build.
- N7. Per-user throughput cap (e.g. 10 push/hour, 5 email/hour, 3 SMS/hour) — spam protection for your users.

---

**3. Estimation**

Event volume
- 30M DAU, each generating 20 notification-worthy events/day → **600M events/day = 6,900 events/sec average**, ~35k/sec peak.
- Recipients per event: a comment on a post with 5k likes → 5,000 in-app + capped pushes. Weight of the distribution is brutal:
  - Assume 1% of events are on content with >10k engaged users. At 35k peak events/sec, 350 events/sec × mean 20k recipients = **7M recipient deliveries/sec**. This is the number that forces the async design.
  - Total daily deliveries: 600M events × mean 3 recipients = **1.8B deliveries/day = 21k/sec average**, ~105k/sec peak.

Storage
- Notification row: `user_id(16) + type(4) + actor_list(ref) + entity_ref(16) + ts(8) + read(1) + channel_state` ≈ **200 B**.
- 1.8B/day × 200 B = **360 GB/day**, 13 TB for 30 days. Inbox needs only 30–90 days → ~40 TB. Columnar for analytics, KV for the inbox.
- The outbox: 105k/sec peak × 500 B per channel message = **52 MB/s** into Kafka.

Bandwidth
- In-app via websocket: 105k deliveries/sec × 1.5 KB (payload + actor metadata) = **157 MB/s** at peak. Fine.
- Push: 105k/sec × 300 B to FCM/APNs = 31 MB/s outbound, but FCM charges and rate-limits per message — the reason for per-user caps.
- SMS: at 3/hour/user cap, 30M users × 3 × 30% open → far below 1M/day; but SMS cost is ~$0.005/message, so the budget constraint is money, not bandwidth.

Availability
- 99.9% = 43.8 min/month of notification loss, which is acceptable; the requirement is that the *triggering action* is unaffected.

Arithmetic to state
- "A celebrity post with 5M likes generates 5M notifications. If I sent a push to each, that is 5M pushes and I would exhaust my per-user quotas and get the app uninstalled. So the only correct design caps per user and groups — 5M likes becomes 5M in-app records, one grouped push, and zero emails."

---

**4. API design**

Internal producer API (from domain services)
```json
// POST /v1/notifications/events   (internal, authenticated)
{
  "eventId": "e_9f3c-uuid",              // idempotency key
  "type": "post.liked",
  "actorIds": ["u_1a2b"],                 // up to 50, then grouped
  "subjectId": "u_owner",                // primary recipient
  "secondaryRecipients": ["u_commenter"], // e.g. thread participants
  "entity": { "type": "post", "id": "p_77", "title": "..." },
  "priority": "normal",
  "createdAt": 1767000000123,
  "channels": ["inapp", "push", "email"]  // optional hint; prefs still win
}
```
→ `202 Accepted { "queued": true, "eventId": "..." }`. Always 202, never 200 with side effects — the caller must not wait.

User-facing
```
GET  /v1/notifications?cursor=&filter=all|mentions|unread      -> inbox page
POST /v1/notifications/{id}/read          -> 204
POST /v1/notifications/read-all            -> 204
GET  /v1/notifications/unread-count       -> {"count": 12}   (cheap, cached)
PUT  /v1/notification-prefs               -> {"post.liked": {"push": false, "email": false}}
PUT  /v1/notification-prefs/quiet-hours   -> {"tz":"America/New_York","start":"22:00","end":"08:00"}
```

Error/degradation cases
| Case | Behavior |
|---|---|
| Event producer times out | producer returns 202 anyway; the action succeeds. **Never fail the user's action because notifications failed.** |
| Unknown recipient | drop with a metric; do not retry |
| Preference service down | fall back to in-app only, `reason="prefs_unavailable"` |
| Channel provider down (FCM/email) | retry with exponential backoff into a per-channel dead-letter queue; other channels unaffected |
| Per-user cap hit | drop the *lowest-priority* notification, increment a counter, never drop a mention |
| Quiet hours | defer to the end of the window with the timezone resolved at send time (not at event time) |
| Duplicate eventId | idempotent no-op, counted |
| Notification older than TTL (24h) | expire un-delivered; do not send a 3-day-old "X liked your post" |

---

**5. Data model**

```
notification_events           -- produced by domain services, outbox pattern
  event_id      UUID PRIMARY KEY
  type, actor_ids, subject_id, entity_ref, created_at
  TTL 7 days
```

```
notification_inbox             -- per-recipient, KV, sharded by user_id
  user_id      BIGINT
  notif_id     BIGINT     -- snowflake
  type         TEXT
  actor_ids    LIST       -- capped at 50, then a ref to a grouped row
  entity_ref   JSONB
  created_at   TIMESTAMPTZ
  read_at      TIMESTAMPTZ NULL
  is_grouped   BOOL
  PRIMARY KEY (user_id, notif_id DESC)
  TTL 30 days
```

```
notification_state            -- per (user, channel) delivery state machine
  user_id, notif_id, channel, state, attempts, next_attempt_at, provider_message_id
  state ENUM(queued, sent, delivered, failed, dropped_suppressed, deferred)
```
Storing state per (user, notif_id, channel) is what makes retries, dedupe, and "did we already email them" possible.

```
notification_group            -- "Lena and 3 others liked your post"
  group_key   = type + entity_ref + actor_bucket
  count       INT
  actor_ids   LIST (last 50)
  updated_at
  TTL 24h
```
group_key must be deterministic so that concurrent likers collapse into one group row.

```
notification_prefs            -- Postgres, small, replicated
  user_id, type, channel, enabled
  + quiet_hours(tz, start, end), global mute
```

```
counters
  user:{id}:push:bucket    -- token bucket, per user per channel
  user:{id}:unread         -- maintained incrementally
```

Access patterns
| Pattern | Served by |
|---|---|
| Inbox read | `notification_inbox` by `(user_id, notif_id)` keyset |
| Unread badge | incremented counter, O(1), never a count query |
| Preference lookup | Postgres replica, cached per-request in-process |
| Per-user throttling | Redis token bucket per `(user, channel)` |
| Grouping | `notification_group` upsert on `group_key` |
| Dedup | `event_id` primary key + `(user_id, notif_id, channel)` state |

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Domain Service (e.g. PostService)
   |  transaction: write post + write notification_events outbox row
   |  return 200/201 to the user IMMEDIATELY
   v
Outbox Relay -> Kafka topic `notifications.events`
   |
   +--> Fanout Service (expand recipients, apply prefs + caps, group, dedupe)
   |      +--> Notification Store (inbox rows)
   |      +--> Channel Router -> per-channel queues
   |             +--> InApp: websocket gateway (look up active sessions)
   |             +--> Push:  FCM / APNs batch worker
   |             +--> Email:  ESP (SendGrid/SES) batch worker
   |             +--> SMS:   provider batch worker
   |             +--> Digest: hourly/daily aggregator -> one summary per user
   |      +--> Throttle: per-user per-channel token buckets in Redis
   |
   +--> Analytics consumer -> columnar sink, funnel metrics (delivered/opened)
```

Read path for in-app
1. Client connects to the websocket gateway with a user token; the gateway registers `(userId, connectionId) -> gatewayId` in Redis with a TTL.
2. The channel router resolves recipients to active connections in one pipelined MGET, filters to supported platforms and app versions, and pushes.
3. No active connection → nothing to do; the inbox row already exists, so the count is correct when the app next opens. This is the elegant property: **the inbox is written once and every channel is an outbound attempt**, so no channel can lose data.
4. Batch per connection: coalesce notifications arriving within 200 ms into one frame.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 The fanout explosion and the three suppression layers**

A like on a post with 5M engagements is 5M notifications. Three layers, applied in order:
1. **Grouping**: within a 24-hour window, all "X liked your post" events on the same entity collapse into one row keyed by `(type, entity, recipient)`, holding a count and the last 50 actors. So 5M likes become 5M *in-app* rows in groups (still one per actor for the *actor's* notification, but one grouped row for the *owner*), one push, one email.
2. **Per-user per-channel caps**: token bucket per `(user, channel)` — push 10/hour, email 5/hour, SMS 3/hour. This is the layer that protects the *recipient* from spam and protects the budget. The cap drops the lowest-priority notification, and mentions/replies always win because they carry `priority: high`.
3. **Channel preference**: the user's explicit setting is authoritative and is checked before anything is enqueued for that channel.

The ordering matters and is worth stating: **prefs first (cheapest, most explicit), then grouping (reduces volume), then caps (bounds spend)**, with the cap deciding *which* notification survives by priority, not by arrival time.

Also: **never notify the actor about their own action**, and suppress notifications for users who muted or blocked the actor. And for a "follow" event, only notify if the actor is not already followed by the recipient (mutual-follow dedupe).

**7.2 Exactly-once perception, not exactly-once delivery**

Exactly-once across a Kafka consumer, an email provider, and an SMTP retry is not achievable. Design for **at-least-once delivery + idempotent rendering**:
- Every event carries a globally unique `eventId`; every notification a `(userId, notifId)`; every channel attempt a `(userId, notifId, channel)` row in `notification_state`.
- Before sending on any channel, check/insert that state row. If it is already `sent`, skip. This is a single-row upsert and it is the dedupe.
- Providers give you idempotency keys (`client-request-id` for FCM/SendGrid) — pass `notifId` as theirs, so a retry after an ambiguous timeout does not duplicate.
- User-facing dedupe: the inbox is a set keyed by `notifId`, and the client renders by `notifId`, so even a duplicate push results in one row.
- Consumer side: process at-least-once with manual offset commit *after* the state write; on crash, replay is safe.
- The residual you must accept and be honest about: a duplicate *email* is possible if the provider accepted the message and then the response was lost and we retried without the idempotency key. Mitigate with a `provider_message_id` reconciliation sweep at 24 hours. That is the honest boundary.

**7.3 Quiet hours, timezones, and the deferral queue**

Three levels, and the middle one is the trap:
1. **Global DND** per channel, or a global mute that stops all non-critical notifications.
2. **Per-channel quiet hours** with a timezone. The trap: the user's timezone changes, or a user travels, and a deferred notification computed at *event* time lands at 3 a.m. Fix: store the raw event and the *earliest eligible send time* (`max(eventTime, nextWindowStart(now, tz))`), and re-resolve the window at send time. Also handle DST transitions by storing the window as local wall-clock plus a tz id, not as UTC offsets.
3. **Per-category quiet hours**: DMs can ring at 2 a.m., marketing cannot. That is a preference, not a schedule.

Deferral mechanics: a `deferred` state with `next_attempt_at` set to the end of the window, driven by a scheduler that scans due items (a time-bucketed index, not a full scan). Deferred notifications get a TTL — an email queued for 3 hours at 2 a.m. is often worse than not sending it, so cap at 12 hours and mark stale ones dropped.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

What breaks first
1. **Push provider rate limits and cost.** FCM caps throughput per project; one campaign or one event class can exhaust it. Fixes: per-channel isolation (each channel has its own pool and quota), a global push budget, and channel fallback (push → in-app only).
2. **SMS cost and A2P 10DLC registration** — a compliance dependency that can silently block delivery.
3. **The websocket session registry** at 30M concurrent connections (see §12.5.4); if it is down, push is doing all the work, which raises cost and lowers engagement simultaneously.
4. **Preference-service dependency on the fanout path.** One slow replica and fanout latency becomes preference latency. Fix: cache prefs per-request and use a stale-while-revalidate cache with a hard 50 ms cap.
5. **Per-user throttle Redis** at 105k deliveries/sec × 3 channels = 315k token ops/sec. One cluster, sharded by user. Or make the caps local/approximate and reconcile.
6. **Grouping row contention**: all 5M likers update the same `group_key` row. Fix: shard the group row by `hash(group_key) % N` with N ≈ 64 so concurrent upserts spread, then merge on read.
7. **Email deliverability**: bouncing or spam-flagged sends destroy a sending reputation permanently. Fix: bounce handling, complaint suppression, and a per-recipient suppression list checked *before* every send.
8. **Digest computation cost** over users with thousands of unread items.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Invariant |
|---|---|---|
| Notification service down entirely | events buffer in Kafka; actions still succeed | the user's action never fails |
| Fanout lag | notifications arrive late; the inbox shows them eventually | add `createdAt` and let the UI show "new" badges |
| Preference service down | in-app only, `reason=prefs_unavailable` | never send a channel you did not verify; err toward *not* sending push |
| FCM down | push fails to DLQ; email/in-app continue | per-channel isolation |
| Email ESP down | retries with backoff; digest absorbs | exponential backoff capped, then DLQ |
| Redis (sessions) down | no websocket push; app-open fetches inbox | push continues but costs more; alert on the ratio |
| Duplicate event from a producer retry | `eventId` dedupe no-op | idempotent producers required (outbox pattern) |
| Bad actor batch (5M recipients) | rate-limited by per-user caps; work shed | caps are global, not per-event |
| Timezone DB change | a window resolves oddly for a few users | re-resolve at send time, not event time |
| Escaping bug in a payload template | user-visible garbage | template rendering is sandboxed + previewed in CI |
| Legal takedown of an entity | notifications referencing it must disappear | notification cleanup subscribes to the same removal events as the content store |

The invariant worth stating first: **the notification system is a lossy, best-effort, at-least-once system, and that is a design property, not a defect.** Every one of its failures is invisible to the action that caused it.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Generation | outbox → Kafka, fully async | synchronous send in the request | 5M-recipient events would blow the 100 ms write budget |
| Aggregation | inbox row is the source of truth; channels are outbound attempts | per-channel independent stores | the inbox can never lose a notification |
| Fanout | expand recipients at the fanout service | push at the domain service | decouples the hot write path from notification cost |
| Grouping | deterministic `group_key` upsert | per-event rows | 5M likes → 1 push |
| Suppression order | prefs → grouping → caps | caps first | caps are the bluntest tool and should be last |
| Cap selection | by priority | by arrival time | a mention must never be dropped in favor of a like |
| Timing | deferral computed and re-resolved at send time | window computed at event time | DST, travel, and stale-nightly-email problems |
| Delivery | per-channel queues with independent DLQs | one shared queue | a dead provider must not block other channels |
| Dedupe | state row + provider idempotency key | exactly-once semantics | exactly-once does not exist across providers |
| Digest | batch aggregate | send everything | caps require a batching path to be useful |
| Precedence | user preference always wins | engagement optimization | engagement-optimized notification volume causes uninstalls |

Rejected wholesale
- **One queue for all channels.** A single slow provider becomes a global backlog, and per-channel retries/failure policies conflict.
- **Sending push for every like on your own post.** Unsubscribe rate becomes the top metric, and it is unrecoverable quickly.
- **Email directly from a domain (no ESP).** Deliverability is a decade of accumulated reputation work; do not start from zero.
- **A synchronous "notify" step in the transaction.** One provider outage becomes a write outage. The outbox pattern exists precisely to prevent this.
- **Storing notification payloads in a relational table and querying by `created_at`.** Correct but unindexable at 21k deliveries/sec; it must be a per-user ordered store.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "A post gets 5 million likes. Walk me through exactly what the system does."**
Model answer: Nothing inline. The like service writes the like and an outbox row — `eventId`, `type: post.liked`, `actorIds`, `entityRef` — and returns to the user in single-digit milliseconds. The relay publishes to Kafka, and the fanout service expands recipients, which is where the explosion actually gets handled. For the post owner it is one event with a count, not 5M events, so grouping collapses it: the `group_key = post.liked + postId + ownerId` row is upserted with a counter and the latest 50 actor ids, and because the key is deterministic, 5M concurrent likers all converge on one row — I shard that row 64 ways to avoid the write hotspot. The owner then gets: one in-app inbox entry per actor so the row is real and countable, one grouped push, and at most one email, all three subject to their per-user per-channel token buckets. For each of the other 4,999,999 likers, the recipient is the post owner and the actor is themselves, so it is one in-app entry and nothing else. I never generate a notification telling someone they liked a post. The crucial constraint is that none of this is in the request path, so the like itself stays fast, and the per-user caps mean even a legitimate viral event cannot spend more than a fixed budget per person per hour.

**Q2. "How do you guarantee a user doesn't get the same email twice?"**
Model answer: I do not guarantee it, and pretending otherwise is the mistake — exactly-once across Kafka, an ESP, and SMTP retries does not exist. What I guarantee is at-most-once *per channel* by construction plus idempotent rendering. Before any send, I upsert a row in `notification_state` keyed by `(user_id, notif_id, channel)`; if it is already `sent`, I skip, and that single-row upsert is the dedupe. I pass `notifId` as the ESP's and FCM's client-request-id so that if a timeout is ambiguous and we retry, the provider collapses it. On the consumer side I commit offsets only after the state write, so a crash replays and hits the dedupe. The inbox is keyed by `notifId` and the client renders by `notifId`, so even a duplicated push produces one row. The honest residual: if the provider accepted a message and the response was lost and we retry without the idempotency key surviving on their side, a duplicate email can happen — so I also run a 24-hour reconciliation that compares sent rows against provider message ids and flags any anomaly. And for user-visible channels I add a belt-and-braces filter: identical content within a 5-minute window is not rendered twice.

**Q3. "How do you implement quiet hours across timezones?"**
Model answer: Three levels, and the middle one is where people get it wrong. A global mute stops everything non-critical. Per-channel quiet hours are stored as local wall-clock time plus a timezone id — never as UTC offsets, because offsets change with DST. And per-category windows, so direct messages can ring at 2 a.m. while marketing cannot, which is really a preference rather than a schedule. The trap is computing the deferred send time when the event is created. If a user in Berlin comments at 01:00 and their window is 22:00–08:00, the notification is deferred to 08:00 local — and if they fly to San Francisco in the meantime, 08:00 Berlin has already passed and the notification either goes out at the wrong hour or is silently lost. So I store the event and the *earliest eligible* time, then re-resolve the window against the user's *current* timezone at send time, using a scheduler driven by a time-bucketed index rather than a full scan. And I cap deferral at 12 hours: a "someone liked your comment" email delivered at 6 a.m. the next morning performs worse than not delivering it, so stale items are dropped and counted.

**Q4. "Which channel gets dropped first when you're overloaded?"**
Model answer: Push first, then SMS, and never in-app — because in-app is the cheap, reliable, already-in-the-inbox channel and dropping it loses data rather than just timeliness. SMS goes early on cost and on A2P registration risk: a message that gets rejected for compliance is not merely undelivered, it burns the sending reputation for every number on the account. Push is the first victim because it is the most expensive per message after SMS, it has the strictest provider rate limits, and it is the most duplicative — the user will see the notification when they open the app anyway. Email is protected by its per-user cap and by being batchable, so I let email queue and drain rather than dropping it. The mechanism is not a hand-written priority list per overload level; it is a global token-bucket budget per channel with per-user sub-buckets underneath, so shedding happens at the recipient level, where it is least visible, rather than by dropping whole events. And critically, shedding is *post* grouping and *post* preference: I never drop a mention to preserve a like, and I never send a channel the user has not verified they want.

**Q5. "How would you let a user rebuild their entire notification settings?"**
Model answer: With a single settings document rather than per-setting rows, because the read path is "give me the effective config for this user" and it happens on every event. `notification_prefs` is a versioned JSON document per user — a map of `type → {inapp, push, email, sms}` plus quiet hours, digest cadence, and a global mute — in Postgres with an in-process cache of 5 minutes and a `prefs_version` on every notification event so a stale reader is detectable. `PUT` bumps the version and publishes an invalidation, so the change is visible in the fanout path within about 5 seconds, which is the real requirement: a user who just muted "follows" should not keep receiving follows. To make the API pleasant I offer two layers: a sparse document where only non-defaults are set, so a user can express "everything on except SMS" without enumerating forty types, plus a `defaults` version that ships with the client so it can render an accurate UI offline. Changing defaults applies prospectively only — retroactively mutating everyone's stored preferences would be a support incident. And "reset to default" is a version bump, not a delete, so the audit trail survives.

**Q6. "How do you test and observe this system at scale?"**
Model answer: Property-based and replay-based testing, because this is a system where bugs are silent. First, determinism: every event carries an `eventId`, so I can replay a day of captured Kafka traffic at 100× rate into a shadow environment and assert on outcomes — total notifications generated, per-user caps respected, no duplicate channel sends, no event delivered after its TTL. That single replay harness catches the majority of bugs, including the ones that only appear under concurrency. Second, invariants as tests: prefs are checked before caps, caps are checked before grouping, at-most-once per channel, never notify the actor, no send during quiet hours. Third, a chaos test that kills each provider mid-batch and asserts the other channels are unaffected and the failed one lands in its DLQ with correct backoff. Fourth, capacity tests that push a 5M-recipient synthetic event and confirm the *triggering action* latency is unaffected — that is the invariant I most need to protect. In production, the metrics I actually alert on are per-channel queue lag, send-success rate, DLQ depth, push-to-inbox ratio (a proxy for whether the websocket path is healthy), unsub-attributed-to-notification rate, and duplicate-suppression count. And the one business metric that predicts future churn better than any technical metric is notification-driven uninstall rate, which is why the per-user caps exist at all.

---

## 12.5.8 YouTube / video streaming

⚫ Expert · **Deep dive**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design YouTube. Users upload 2-hour 4K video, it becomes a streamable asset worldwide, and playback must start in under 2 seconds and not buffer."

The score is in: upload (chunked, resumable, transcoded to an **ABR ladder**), the difference between **live and VOD** delivery, why **HTTP progressive download with a byte-range manifest** beats HLS for VOD, CDN + cache hierarchy with content-addressed keys, and playback QoE instrumentation.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Upload: up to 20 GB, resumable, chunked, progress, client-side retry.
- F2. Transcode to multiple resolutions/bitrates (the ABR ladder), generate thumbnails, preview sprite, captions.
- F3. Playback with adaptive bitrate, seeking, resume position.
- F4. Live streaming ingest (RTMP/SRT → ingest → packaging → distribution).
- F5. Metadata, view counts, likes, comments, subscriptions, watch history.
- F6. Recommendation: related videos, up-next, autoplay.
- F7. Captions, audio tracks, playback speed, picture-in-picture.
- F8. DRM for licensed content.
- NFR
- N1. Playback startup: **< 2 s** to first frame globally (TTFF).
- N2. Rebuffer ratio **< 0.5%** of session; startup delay p95 < 2 s.
- N3. Availability 99.95%+ for playback; upload is asynchronous and may queue.
- N4. Upload throughput: 500 concurrent uploads × 50 Mbps = **~3 GB/s ingest** at peak.
- N5. Storage: video is the dominant cost — see arithmetic.
- N6. Global coverage with < 100 ms TTFF from any region.

---

**3. Estimation**

Content volume
- 500 hours of uploaded video per minute → **720,000 hours/day = 259M seconds/day**.
- Average encode bitrate across the ladder for a 1080p-ish source ≈ 6 Mbps total.
- 259M s × 6 Mbps ÷ 8 = **194 GB/day of encoded output** ≈ 71 TB/year. Source uploads are far larger: at 100 Mbps average, **3.2 TB/day**.
- Storage at $0.023/GB (S3 Glacier-ish tier) for a 2-year retention: 2 years × 71 TB = 142 TB → **$3,270/month**; hot (S3 Standard, 30 days) 5.8 TB → $130/month. The asymmetry between "hot 30 days, cold 2 years" is a real, quotable cost decision.

Playback bandwidth
- Assume 2B daily views, average 8 Mbps delivered, 10 minutes average watch:
  - 2e9 × 600 s = **1.2 × 10¹² seconds streamed/day**.
  - 1.2e12 × 8 Mbps ÷ 8 = **1.2 PB/day egress** ≈ **13.9 MB/s** average? No — 1.2e15 bytes/day ÷ 86,400 = **13.9 GB/s** ≈ **111 Gbps**. This is the single most important number in the design: **the bitrate × watch-time product dominates everything.**
  - Per-peak: 8× average = **111 Gbps**, ~1.6 Tbps with 4K mix.
- Cost: at $0.04/GB, 1.2 PB/day = **$48k/day = $1.44M/month**. Conclusion: **egress is the business**, which is why there is a video business model at all, and why every optimization below is really a cost optimization.

Transcoding cost
- 500 hours of video/minute. Transcoding at ~0.2× realtime per 1080p stream on a GPU gives 500/0.2 = 2,500 concurrent transcode slots at peak. Each slot ~$0.30/hour → **$1,800/hour = $43k/month**. Transcoding is the second-largest cost after egress, and it is why the ladder is capped at ~6 renditions.

Storage/asset breakdown per video
| Asset | Per hour of video | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| Source | ~45 GB | archival, re-encode |
| Renditions | ~2.7 GB (6 Mbps) | playback |
| HLS/DASH segments (TS) | ~4 GB | packaging + DRM |
| fMP4 (CMAF) | ~4 GB | for browsers with MSE |
| Thumbnails + sprites | ~20 MB | scrub bar, preview |
| Captions | ~50 KB | accessibility |

Metadata
- Video row: ~1 KB. 720,000 hours/day at ~10-minute average = 1M uploads/day = **1 GB/day**. Trivial.

Arithmetic to show
- "Egress is 111 Gbps and storage is 71 TB/year. A 1% reduction in average bitrate saves more money than every other optimization combined. So the ladder design, the per-title encoding ladder (rather than per-user), and CDN cache hit rate are the design."

---

**4. API design**

Upload (resumable, client-driven)
```
POST /upload/v1/session?size=...&contentType=video/mp4
-> { "sessionId": "s_...", "parts": [ {"partNumber":1, "url":"<presigned PUT>"}, ... ],
     "partSizeBytes": 16777216, "expiresAt": "..." }

POST /upload/v1/session/{id}/complete   { "parts": [{"partNumber":1,"etag":"..."}] }
-> 202 { "videoId": "v_...", "status": "uploaded", "processing": true }
```
- `GET /upload/v1/session/{id}?parts=1..N` → which parts landed, so the client resumes rather than restarting.
- `POST /upload/v1/video/{id}/publish` → user metadata, then the asset becomes available.

Playback
```
GET /playback/v1/video/{videoId}
-> { "videoId":"v_...", "status":"ready",
     "renditions": [ {"id":"r_240p","width":426,"height":240,"bitrateKbps":400,"codec":"avc1.4d401e","mimeType":"video/mp4"},
                     {"id":"r_1080p", ... "mimeType":"video/mp4"} ],
     "audioTracks": [ {"id":"a_en","codec":"mp4a.40.2","language":"en"} ],
     "captions":    [ {"id":"c_en","format":"vtt","url":"https://.../c_en.vtt"} ],
     "drm": [ {"keySystem":"com.widevine.alpha","licenseUrl":"https://..."} ],
     "durationMs": 7420000, "posterUrl": "https://cdn.../poster.jpg",
     "storyboardUrl": "https://cdn.../sprite.jpg", "engagement": {...} }

GET https://cdn.../{videoId}/{renditionId}/segment-{n}.m4s     -> 200 (byte-range served)
GET https://cdn.../{videoId}/manifest.mpd                       -> DASH manifest
GET https://cdn.../{videoId}/index.m3u8                        -> HLS master playlist
GET https://api/cdn/cdn-dash/video/{videoId}/seg-1.m4s         -> byte-range on a single file
```

Live
```
POST /live/v1/broadcasts            -> { "ingestUrl": "rtmps://a.rtmps.youtube.com/live2/x?key=...", "streamKey": "..." }
POST /live/v1/broadcasts/{id}/end
GET  /live/v1/broadcasts/{id}/status -> { "state":"live", "viewers": 18422, "startHealth":"good" }
```

Error cases
| Case | Code | Behavior |
|---|---|---|
| Part upload failed | per-part | client retries only that 16 MB part; resumable by design |
| Session expired | 410 | client starts a new session but keeps already-uploaded parts if they are still referenced |
| Codec unsupported | 422 | reject at session creation, not after 3 hours of upload |
| Transcode failed for one rendition | 200 | publish with the surviving renditions; alert; retry the failed rung |
| Video not ready | 202 with `status: "processing"`, `etaSeconds` | 200 + playable if the 240p rendition is ready (progressive availability) |
| Playback URL expired | 403 | regenerate signed URLs |
| Watch-history write conflict | 202 | last-write-wins per (user, video); the client owns the truth locally |
| Live ingest unhealthy | 503 on ingest | broadcaster's encoder auto-reconnects with backoff |
| Region blocked (DRM/geo) | 403 | do not leak the CDN URL |

---

**5. Data model**

```
videos
  video_id          UUID PRIMARY KEY
  owner_id          UUID
  title, description TEXT
  visibility        ENUM(private, unlisted, public)
  duration_ms       BIGINT
  source_key        TEXT      -- S3, original upload
  status            ENUM(uploaded, transcoding, ready, failed, blocked)
  published_at      TIMESTAMPTZ
  region_policy     JSONB     -- geo/DRM restrictions
```

```
renditions
  video_id, rendition_id  -- 'r_1080p'
  width, height, bitrate_kbps, codec, profile, level, fps
  container             -- 'mp4' (progressive) | 'fmp4' | 'ts'
  object_key            -- S3 key of the single file (VOD) or segment prefix (live)
  size_bytes            BIGINT
  moov_atom_position    -- 'fast' (front-loaded for fast start) | 'tail'
  created_at
  PRIMARY KEY (video_id, rendition_id)
```
`moov_atom_position: fast` is what makes a progressive MP4 start playing in one round trip instead of two — a concrete, interviewable detail.

```
segments (live only)
  stream_id, rendition_id, seq, duration_ms, object_key, state(ready|cleaned)
```

```
playback_progress
  user_id, video_id, position_ms, updated_at
  PRIMARY KEY (user_id, video_id)
```

```
video_stats
  video_id, window_start, views, watch_seconds, avg_bitrate_selected
  -- 5-min and 1-hour rollups; never a per-event count on the hot path
```

```
encoding_profiles
  name, ladder JSONB, audio_bitrate_kbps, hw_profile, cost_class
```

```
assets
  video_id, kind ENUM(thumbnail|storyboard|caption|audio_track|preview|heatmap),
  object_key, meta JSONB
```

Access patterns
| Pattern | Served by |
|---|---|
| Playback manifest | CDN-cached, seconds TTL; versioned by video + profile version |
| Segment/byte range | CDN, immutable, 1-year TTL |
| Seek | range request on the rendition file; storyboard sprite for the scrub bar |
| Watch history | per-user KV, written on pause/exit/periodic, LWW |
| View count | aggregated into rollups from a batched events stream |
| Hot videos | a single "trending" set refreshed every 5 minutes |

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Upload client
  | chunked multipart, presigned per-part URLs, resumable, client reports progress
  v
Upload Service -> Object Storage (source, multi-region)  -> "uploaded" event
  |
  v
Transcode Orchestrator (Kubernetes jobs, GPU autoscale)
   probe -> validate -> generate ladder -> per-rendition encode (parallel)
   -> audio extract -> thumbnail + storyboard sprite -> captions (ASR + translate)
   -> package (DASH/fMP4 or HLS/TS) -> DRM encrypt where required
   -> publish "ready" (progressive: publish when 240p+360p land)
  |
  v
Playback Service (metadata, manifests, authorization) ----> CDN edge caches
  |                                                            ^
  +--> viewer fetches manifest + segments   --------------------+
  |
  +--> Playback Events -> Kafka -> QoE aggregator -> per-video rollups
  +--> Recommendation (related videos) reads video_stats + embedding ANN
```

Live path, separately
```
Broadcaster encoder -> RTMP/SRT ingest (edge, per region)
   -> ingest cluster -> transcoder (low latency, ~4-6s end to end)
   -> packager (DASH/HLS segmenter, 2-4s segments)
   -> CDN with short TTL (2-3s) on live segments
```
Live and VOD share the CDN and the player, and nothing else. Live segments are **not immutable** (they are cleaned up after a window) and must not be cached for a year.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Upload: resumable chunked upload and why the client owns the bytes**

- Client splits into **16 MB parts**, requests presigned `PUT` URLs for a session, and uploads parts **in parallel (4–8 at a time)** to object storage directly. The app server never proxies bytes. That single decision is why ingest scales: at 20 GB and 100 Mbps, one video takes 27 minutes; a server proxying that would hold 20k concurrent connections.
- **Resumability**: the client records completed part etags locally; on failure it calls `GET /upload/session/{id}` and re-uploads only missing parts. Part-level idempotency makes retries free.
- **Multipart completion must be verified**: compare part count and total size against the declared size, verify checksums, then abort incomplete multipart uploads with a lifecycle rule or you accumulate storage cost invisibly.
- **Validate early**: codec/container probing at session creation (`422` immediately) rather than after a 3-hour upload. Also quota enforcement at session creation.
- **Direct-to-storage upload means no server-side virus scanning of bytes** — irrelevant for video, but it means the storage bucket policy is your only gate, so use a dedicated bucket with no public access and a strict IAM role for the signing service.
- For mobile: HLS-based or chunked resumable upload with a lower part size (1 MB) and adaptive concurrency based on measured throughput.

**7.2 The ABR ladder: per-title encoding is the core cost lever**

A fixed ladder (240p/480p/720p/1080p/1440p/2160p at fixed bitrates) is simple and suboptimal. **Per-title encoding** analyzes each video (motion, complexity, film grain, scene changes) and picks a bitrate–resolution ladder optimized for that content, so a talking-head video at 720p uses 1.2 Mbps while a sports highlight at 1080p uses 4.5 Mbps — same perceived quality, materially less egress.
- The ladder is computed per title by a job, then baked into the manifest.
- Codecs: AV1/VP9 where the client supports it (typically 25–35% bitrate saving) with H.264 fallback; H.265 for Apple hardware. Manifest carries both and the player picks.
- **Cap the ladder at ~6 renditions** for a fixed-width ladder plus 2 AVC and 2 AV1 fallbacks. Every rendition costs transcode time, storage, CDN cache entries, and manifest bytes.
- Audio: separate track, 64–128 kbps AAC, or Opus at 64 kbps.
- The result to state: **a 25% average bitrate reduction at 2B views/day is roughly $350k/month of egress**, which dwarfs most engineering optimizations. That framing turns a media-technology detail into a business insight.
- Also: **CMAF/fMP4 for browsers** (single file, byte-range requests, MSE) and **TS/HLS for Safari** (native HLS). Supporting both is the reason for the `container` field. And front-load the `moov` atom for MP4 so playback starts after one request.

**7.3 Delivery: CDN hierarchy, caching, and cache keys**

Hierarchy
```
Viewer -> ISP/edge PoP cache (200+ locations) -> regional CDN cache -> origin (S3 via CDN origin shield)
```
- **Content-addressed, immutable URLs**: `/{videoId}/{profileVersion}/{renditionId}/file.mp4` with `Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable`. Because the key embeds the profile version, a re-encode produces new URLs and **invalidation is never needed**. This is the same immutability trick as Pastebin (§12.5.2), applied at media scale.
- **Origin shield**: a regional cache between CDN and S3, so a miss storm hits one origin, not 200.
- **Cache hit rate is the metric.** Popular videos should be ~99%+; the long tail is where misses hurt, mitigated by prefetching the top-N videos of each region into the shield cache on publish.
- **Range requests** are essential for seeking; ensure the CDN and origin support them and that small ranges do not get cached as separate objects (cache the whole file, serve ranges from it).
- **Prefetch next video**: when the player is within ~10 s of the end (or on `readyState` HAVE_FUTURE_DATA), prefetch the first segment of the next autoplay video. This is a 15–25% reduction in startup delay for autoplay-heavy sessions, and it is one of the highest-leverage client-side changes.
- **Cache the manifest** for seconds, not a year, but include the profile version in the URL so it can be longer if you want.
- **Signed URLs** for private/unlisted content: short TTL, path-scoped signature, and no directory listing on the bucket.

**7.4 VOD vs live: what actually differs**

| Aspect | VOD | Live |
|---|---|---|
| Packaging | one file per rendition, or a full segment set | continuous segmenting, 2–4 s segments |
| Cache TTL | 1 year, immutable | 2–3 s, and segments are deleted after ~10 min |
| Origin | object storage | packager/edge ingest cluster |
| Latency budget | seconds (startup) | glass-to-glass **< 6 s** for most, < 3 s for low-latency mode |
| Encoder | offline, quality-optimized, GPU farm | real-time, low-latency presets, scaling constraints |
| Failure | retry the job | broadcaster reconnects with backoff; failover ingest node |
| Authorization | signed URL | signed URL + periodic revalidation |
| Cost | storage + egress | ingest + egress (storage tiny) |

Low-latency live (LL-HLS/LL-DASH) uses partial segments (100–200 ms chunks) to cut latency to ~2–3 s at the cost of complexity and worse caching. Say it exists and that most live streams do not need it.
Critical rule: **never cache live segments long**, and never let a live URL share a cache key with VOD.

**7.5 Playback QoE: the metrics that tell you the truth**

- **Startup**: time to first byte, time to first frame (TTFF), rebuffer-before-start. TTFF target p75 < 1.5 s, p95 < 2.5 s.
- **Rebuffer**: rebuffer ratio (seconds stalled / seconds playing) target < 0.5%; stall count; stall duration distribution.
- **Bitrate**: average selected bitrate, **bitrate-switch count and magnitude** (thrashing is a bug, not a network problem), and **buffer occupancy** — the leading indicator. Falling buffer predicts a stall 5–10 s early, which is your window to act.
- **Watch quality proxy**: fraction of session at less than 70% of the source's average bitrate. This is a better product metric than raw stall rate because it is about perceived quality.
- **Seek**: seek latency and post-seek stall rate; seeks into an uncached region are the worst case.
- Instrument per session, sampled at 1%, and join to the CDN response headers (`X-Cache: HIT/MISS`, PoP id) so you can attribute a stall to a specific PoP. **Attribution is the difference between "the CDN is bad" and "ASN 1234's peering with PoP 22 is bad."**
- Connect these to business outcomes: watch time, and retention at day 7, segmented by startup-time bucket. That analysis is how you prove a 100 ms startup improvement is worth the money.

**7.6 Copyright, DRM, and the enforcement surface**

- **Content ID / fingerprinting**: fingerprint at ingest (audio + video), store matches, and apply policy automatically (block, monetize, or allow with a claim). A fingerprint lookup per upload, not per playback.
- **DRM**: Widevine/FairPlay/PlayReady, license service with per-title keys, `outputProtection=HDCP` handling, key rotation, and **license requests are the security boundary** — do not let the CDN serve license responses.
- **Geo-blocking and takedowns**: a removal must invalidate CDN copies. With immutable URLs you cannot purge by content hash cheaply at 200 PoPs — so serve playback metadata through a service that can 403 quickly, and push a purge task for the specific `videoId` prefix. This is a genuine trade-off of immutable caching and should be stated honestly.
- **Upload scanning** and per-region compliance requirements (COPPA, GDPR flags, restricted-content classification).

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

What breaks first
1. **Egress**, always. 111 Gbps average and ~1.6 Tbps peak. Every optimization here is a cost optimization.
2. **Transcode capacity.** 500 hours/minute at 0.2× realtime needs ~2,500 concurrent slots, and GPU availability is the constraint in most regions. Fixes: bursty autoscaling + spot capacity + queue depth as the autoscaling signal + per-title optimization to reduce the total work.
3. **Upload origin bandwidth.** 3.2 TB/day ingest; a regional S3 endpoint saturates at a few Gbps, so uploads must be direct-to-storage with multi-region endpoints and multipart parallelism.
4. **Manifest and metadata service QPS.** One metadata call per playback start; at 2B views/day that is 23k/s average, ~180k/s peak. Must be CDN-cached with a short TTL; the authorization check is the only non-cacheable part.
5. **Long-tail cache miss storms.** A new or regionally-unknown video's first requests all miss. Fixes: prefetch to origin shield, warm on publish.
6. **Hot video origin overload** when a video is trending and every PoP misses simultaneously. Fix: pre-warm + shield + rate limit.
7. **DRM license QPS** on big premieres; needs its own autoscaled pool.
8. **Recommendation fanout** — related videos for every playback is an ANN lookup plus popularity blending, at 180k QPS. A separate service with its own cache.
9. **Live ingest peaks**: 200k concurrent live streams at peak with per-stream state. Fixes: autoscaling packagers, per-stream sharding, and a hard cap on concurrent live streams per region.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Transcoder unavailable | new videos stay `processing`; **playback of existing content is unaffected** | queue in Kafka; ETA shown to the uploader; progressive publish of low renditions |
| One rendition fails | publish remaining ladder | retry that rung; alert; never block the whole video |
| CDN PoP failure | nearby PoP serves; some slowdown | anycast; manifest includes fallback URLs |
| Origin shield down | every miss goes to S3 → S3 throttles | shield is highly available with N+1; S3 request-rate limits are a real risk |
| Hot video miss storm | slow startup for everyone | pre-warm, add renditions to the shield, TTL-based pinning |
| S3 region failure | playback of assets replicated there fails | multi-region buckets + a manifest that references the healthy region |
| DRM license service down | playback fails only for protected titles | separate pool; alert; do not silently serve clear |
| Live ingest node dies | broadcaster reconnects | encoder backoff; standby ingest in another region |
| Recommendation service down | skip related videos; continue playback | playback must never depend on it |
| Playback metadata service down | cached manifests serve with a 5-min stale window; beyond that, playback fails | cache is the safety net; replication |
| Watch-history write down | playback continues; progress lost | buffer client-side, batch on reconnect |
| Copyright strike | title must vanish | playback service returns 403 immediately + asynchronous purge; accepts a purge delay for immutable CDN copies |
| Codec regression in a new player build | stalls for a slice of traffic | canary by app version; feature flag the codec preference |

The invariant: **playback of published content never depends on the upload/transcode pipeline.** Those are separate systems with separate SLOs, and coupling them is the classic mistake.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Upload path | direct-to-storage presigned parts | proxy through app server | 20 GB through a service is absurd; kills ingest scale |
| Part size | 16 MB (1 MB on mobile) | one 20 GB PUT | resume granularity vs request overhead |
| Packaging | CMAF/fMP4 for browsers, HLS/TS for Safari | HLS everywhere | MSE gives byte-range seeking and better rebuffer control; HLS is needed for Safari |
| Bitrate strategy | per-title encoding ladder | fixed global ladder | 20–35% egress reduction at the same quality |
| Codec | AV1/VP9 + H.264 fallback | H.264 only | AV1 is ~30% cheaper in bits, with a compatibility fallback |
| Renditions | ~6-8, capped | one rendition per standard resolution | each rendition costs transcode, storage, cache entries, manifest bytes |
| Cache | immutable, content-addressed, 1-year TTL | short TTL + explicit purge | invalidation becomes unnecessary; the cost is purge difficulty on takedowns |
| VOD delivery | progressive/range over a single file for VOD | segmented HLS for VOD | fewer requests, better seeking, simpler CDN; segments only for live or DRM-heavy cases |
| Live segments | 2–3 s TTL, deleted after ~10 min | long TTL | storage growth and serving deleted live content |
| Seek | range request + storyboard sprite | per-segment granularity | cheap scrubbing without a segment index on the client |
| Transcode platform | managed GPU fleet with spot | long-lived GPU servers | 40% cost reduction at the cost of possible preemption handling |
| Related videos | separate ANN + popularity service | inline lookup in playback | keeps playback independent of recommendation |
| Metadata | CDN-cached manifests with short TTL | per-request metadata DB read | 180k QPS needs the cache |

Rejected wholesale
- **Streaming video through a single application server.** Bandwidth per server kills at a few Gb/s.
