- **Storing video in the relational database.** Object storage, always.
- **HLS segments with hour-long files for VOD.** Segments are for live; for VOD a single file with range requests is simpler and better.
- **One global "best" bitrate for all videos.** Ignores content complexity; either wastes bits or looks bad.
- **Watermarking as the primary DRM.** It is a deterrent, not a protection; use real DRM.
- **Recommendation inline in the playback request.** One flaky dependency breaks the most important page in the product.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "A 20 GB upload fails at 90%. Walk me through what happens."**
Model answer: Nothing restarts. The client uploads 16 MB parts in parallel to presigned URLs, so at 90% it has a set of completed parts recorded with their etags, plus whatever was in flight. It calls `GET /upload/session/{id}`, gets the list of parts the service believes landed, reconciles with the local record, and re-uploads only the missing parts — which is 2 GB, not 20 GB, so it resumes in roughly 10 minutes instead of 27. Two details matter. First, part uploads are idempotent, so a retry of a part that actually succeeded just overwrites it, which is why I keep the part size at 16 MB rather than going to 256 MB chunks: at 256 MB, a failure means re-uploading 256 MB, and at 100 Mbps on a mobile connection that variance is brutal. On mobile I drop to 1 MB parts and reduce concurrency to 2–3. Second, I have an S3 multipart lifecycle rule that aborts incomplete uploads after 7 days, because otherwise every abandoned upload leaves billable parts behind forever — that is a real and common leak. I also validate codec and quota at session creation, before any bytes move, because discovering an unsupported codec after 27 minutes is a support incident. Finally, the client reports progress to the app so the user sees resume, not a restart.

**Q2. "How do you get playback to start in under 2 seconds globally?"**
Model answer: Five things. One, the manifest and metadata response is CDN-cached for seconds, so playback start does not touch my origin. Two, the rendition files are progressive MP4 with the `moov` atom front-loaded, so the player gets metadata and the first bytes in one round trip instead of downloading to the end of the file to find the index — that single packaging change is often several hundred milliseconds. Three, the first segment is small; for CMAF I use 1–2 s segments and for progressive I rely on the byte-range path so the player can start on a sub-range request. Four, warm the CDN: on publish, and periodically for regionally-popular videos, prefetch the origin shield so the first viewer does not eat a cold miss, and use aggressive prefetch of the *next* autoplay video's first bytes once `readyState` reaches `HAVE_FUTURE_DATA`. Five, measure and attribute: TTFB, TTFF, and rebuffer-before-start per session, joined against CDN response headers so I can tell PoP from ISP from player. When startup is bad it is almost never the server — it is TLS handshake plus two round trips plus a cold cache at a specific PoP. And I would add that the biggest structural win is player-side: keep a small persistent connection pool, prewarm the next video, and avoid re-resolving the manifest on every playback.

**Q3. "How do you decide how many renditions and what bitrates?"**
Model answer: Per-title, not global. A fixed ladder wastes bits on easy content and starves hard content. The pipeline analyzes the source — motion vectors, scene-cut density, film grain, noise, text overlay — and computes a rate-quality curve, then picks 5–8 points on it. So a static talking head gets 720p at 1.1 Mbps and a sports highlight gets 1080p at 4.8 Mbps, both at the same perceptual quality. That is 20–35% average bitrate reduction, and at 1.2 PB/day of egress it is worth several hundred thousand dollars a month, which is more than the entire transcoding budget for the analysis pipeline. On top of per-title selection: AV1/VP9 as primary with H.264 fallback, typically 30% cheaper in bits for the same quality; separate audio at 64 kbps Opus; and a hard cap on rendition count, because every rendition costs transcode GPU time, storage, cache entries in every PoP, and manifest bytes. I cap at 6 for the ladder plus fallbacks and I would rather have a 300 ms gap in the ladder than a 9th rendition. The last thing I would add is a per-viewer component: even with a perfect ladder, a viewer on a bad Wi-Fi path picks a low rendition, so the ladder's lower rungs need enough headroom to survive 2× bandwidth estimation error.

**Q4. "How does your design differ for live streaming, and what breaks?"**
Model answer: Live shares the CDN and the player with VOD and almost nothing else. Instead of one pre-encoded file per rendition, there is a continuous pipeline: broadcaster encoder to RTMP/SRT, regional ingest, transcoder in low-latency presets, packager slicing into 2–4 second segments, CDN with 2–3 second TTLs, and segments garbage-collected after about ten minutes. Glass-to-glass is 4–6 seconds with that setup; LL-HLS/LL-DASH partial segments of 100–200 ms get to 2–3 seconds at the cost of worse caching and much more complexity, and only a minority of streams need it. What breaks: first, cache correctness — live segments must never be cached long and must never share a cache key with VOD, because serving a stale live segment is a visible freeze; I namespace by `live/{streamId}/{rendition}` and never by video id. Second, transcoder capacity — live transcoding cannot be queued or bursty the way VOD can, so a spike in concurrent streams needs pre-warmed capacity and a hard cap on concurrent streams per region, with 429 to the broadcaster rather than an ever-growing queue. Third, ingest node failure, which the broadcaster's encoder handles by reconnecting with exponential backoff to a different ingest endpoint. Fourth, clock and drift between audio and video, which is a transcoder preset problem rather than an architecture one. And the cost profile flips: storage is negligible, ingest CPU and egress dominate.

**Q5. "How do you measure whether playback quality is actually good?"**
Model answer: Rebuffer ratio is the headline, but it is a lagging indicator, so I lead with buffer occupancy — the player's forward buffer, which falls before a stall happens and gives me 5–10 seconds of warning to shift CDN or reallocate bitrate. Alongside it: TTFF with p75 and p95 targets rather than a mean, since startup matters and averages hide it; stall count and duration distribution, because 10 small stalls is a different experience from 1 long one; average selected bitrate; and bitrate-switch count and magnitude, where frequent switching means the bandwidth estimator is oscillating and that is a client bug, not a network problem. Then the proxy that actually predicts retention: fraction of session spent below 70% of the title's average bitrate, segmented by startup-time bucket. That joins a technical metric to a business outcome, which is how I get budget for a 200 ms improvement. Critically, every metric is joined against CDN response headers so I can attribute a stall to a PoP, an ISP, or a device class — otherwise "the CDN is slow" is unactionable. I sample at ~1% for volume reasons but always sample fully for new app versions, since regressions ship in the player far more often than in the CDN.

**Q6. "A video is removed for copyright. How fast can you make it disappear?"**
Model answer: Two clocks, and I will be honest that one of them is not fast. Fast path: the playback metadata service is the authorization point, so on removal I set `status=blocked` and every manifest request returns 403. Any viewer who asks for playback after that instant gets nothing — that path is seconds, and it covers 100% of new playback starts. Also purge the origin and issue a CDN invalidation for the `videoId` prefix, which for most PoPs is tens of seconds. Slow path: viewers who already have the manifest cached, or bytes already in the player's buffer, keep playing until the manifest TTL or buffer runs out, which is minutes. With immutable content-addressed URLs, purging a specific object at 200 PoPs is inherently slower than mutating it — that is the trade-off I accepted for never needing invalidation in the normal case, and it is the right trade at a million uploads a day, but it is a real trade. I mitigate by keeping the manifest TTL short and by re-validating `blockStatus` on a separate cheap endpoint the player polls during long sessions, so an in-progress 4-hour movie does not keep serving a removed title. And every removal is written to an immutable audit log with actor and legal basis, because a takedown you cannot account for is its own kind of problem.

---

## 12.5.9 Netflix

⚫ Expert · **Deep dive**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design Netflix. 300M subscribers, hundreds of millions of hours of video, watch it anywhere, and it has to play instantly on a TV."

Netflix differs from YouTube in three ways that must show up in the design: it is **almost entirely VOD with a small live tail**, **byte-range requests on single large files rather than segmented streams**, and it has a **strict CDN contract (Open Connect)** that pushes storage into ISP networks — a business and architecture decision more than a technical one. What the interviewer is really testing: multi-DC design, playback QoE at TV scale, and whether you know that the video bytes never touch Netflix's own data centers during steady-state playback.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Browse (rows by genre), search, title detail, "My List", recommendations.
- F2. Playback on TV/phone/web with resume, profiles, watchlist, multiple profiles per account.
- F3. Adaptive bitrate by bandwidth and device capability.
- F4. Live channels (a minority of viewing; provider-originated or sports).
- F5. Downloads for offline on mobile.
- F6. Subtitles, audio description, multiple audio tracks, parental controls.
- F7. DRM (Widevine/FairPlay/PlayReady) with per-profile keys.

Non-functional
- N1. TTFF **< 1.5 s p75 / < 2.5 s p95** on a TV (TVs are slower: they buffer less and their Wi-Fi is worse).
- N2. Rebuffer **< 0.25%** of session — Netflix's target is noticeably tighter than YouTube's.
- N3. Availability 99.9%+ per region; regional failures must not be global.
- N4. ~5,000 concurrent streams per Open Connect appliance; Netflix's published peak is **tens of millions of concurrent streams**.
- N5. Storage: petabytes of video plus multiple renditions.
- N6. CDN fill: Netflix ships **terabytes of appliances per week** into ISP networks.
- N7. Recommendation quality is a business KPI: the top-1 metric is hours viewed, so the ranker matters more than the CDN.

---

**3. Estimation**

Egress
- 300M subscribers, ~2.5 hours/day average, ~70% concurrent peak/off-peak ratio → peak concurrent streams ≈ **200M** (Netflix has publicly cited tens of millions to hundreds of millions).
- Peak bandwidth 4.5 Mbps average per stream → 200M × 4.5 Mbps = **900 Gbps ≈ 112 GB/s** at peak.
- Daily: 300M × 2.5 h × 3600 s × 4.5 Mbps ÷ 8 = **1.2 × 10¹⁶ bytes ≈ 12 PB/day**. That is ~5× YouTube's daily egress for a fraction of the watch time — because TV streams run at higher bitrates for longer sessions.
- Cost at $0.02/GB (Netflix negotiates CDN rates far below retail): **$240k/day ≈ $7M/month**. Cost per subscriber per month ≈ $23 of egress alone, against ~$15 ARPU. This is exactly why Netflix owns the CDN: to move that number off the public-internet price sheet.

Storage
- ~100M titles of ~2 hours average. Rendition ladder at ~5 Mbps total → 2 h × 3600 s × 5 Mbps ÷ 8 = **4.5 GB per title per rendition set** → **450 TB** total. Source masters far larger.
- Fill rate: with 200 PoPs × 200 TB per PoP = **40 PB of deployed storage** for a 450 TB working set — an ~90× replication factor, which is the key insight: **the media working set is small enough to fully replicate everywhere**, so the origin is rarely touched.

Transcoding
- Netflix ingests at low bitrate already (a 5 Mbps "mezzanine"), so the ladder is cheaper than YouTube's. ~1,000 titles/day → trivial vs. YouTube's 500 hours/minute.

Arithmetic to state
- "A 450 TB working set replicated across 200 PoPs is 90 PB of deployed appliances. That is only affordable because the asset set is small relative to the reach. If the catalog were 50× larger, this model collapses into a CDN-with-misses and the cost structure inverts." That is the honest strategic framing of Open Connect.

---

**4. API design**

`GET /video/{titleId}/manifests/{profile,device}` → DASH/HLS manifest with renditions and a **heartbeat field**:
```json
{
  "titleId": "t_80123456",
  "availability": {"start": 1767000000, "end": 1767600000, "expireAfterDownload": false},
  "mediaPresentationDuration": "PT2H11M4S",
  "renditions": [
    {"id":"r_720p","width":1280,"height":720,"bandwidth":2400000,
     "mimeType":"video/mp4","codecs":"avc1.4d401f","audio":"a_en"},
    {"id":"r_1080p","bandwidth":4800000, ...}
  ],
  "drm": [{"schemeIdUri":"urn:mpeg:dash:mp4protection:2011","value":"<widevine>"}],
  "tracks": [{"kind":"text","label":"English","url":"https://.../t_en.vtt"}],
  "cdnEndPoints": ["https://nflxvideo.net/..."],
  "heartbeatMs": 10000,          // server can revoke playback
  "licenseUrl": "https://api.netflix.com/license"
}
```
- The **heartbeat** is a Netflix-specific and interview-worthy detail: the manifest declares that the client must re-check periodically, so a revoked title stops within ~10 s rather than after the manifest TTL.
- **Loyalty/edge architecture**: Open Connect appliances do DNS-based traffic steering; the player hits the closest appliance and the appliance serves from local disk, or the CDN cache. There is no Netflix origin in the playback path at steady state.
- `GET /profiles/{profileId}` → watch position, My List, continue watching.
- `POST /playback/{titleId}/heartbeat` → every 10 s while playing: position, bitrate, rebuffer, error codes. This is also the DRM/authorization heartbeat and the QoE data source.
- `GET /search?q=&row=` → separate service, Elasticsearch-like index.
- `POST /downloads/{titleId}` → encrypted offline license + file.
- Error cases: `410 Gone` for expired licensing heartbeat, `403` for out-of-house playback policy, `429` for per-device stream limits (Netflix limits concurrent streams per account), `404` for geo-unavailable, and `206 Partial Content` for every byte range.

---

**5. Data model**

```
titles
  title_id, title_type, release_year, runtime_ms, maturity_rating
  available_from, available_to, geo_blocklist
  PRIMARY KEY (title_id)
```
```
renditions (asset manifest)
  title_id, rendition_id, width, height, bandwidth_bps, codec, audio_track_id,
  segment_offsets JSONB     -- precomputed byte ranges for seeking
  file_size_bytes, cdn_key
  PRIMARY KEY (title_id, rendition_id)
```
```
profiles          user_id, profile_id, name, is_kids, playback_restrictions, max_concurrent_streams
profile_state     profile_id, title_id, position_ms, updated_at, last_played
viewership        (title_id, date, hour) -> minutes_viewed, starts, completion_rate
```
```
recommendations  model version, profile_id, candidate set, scores   (offline, materialized)
```
```
housekeeping      Open Connect appliance inventory, per-ISP fill level, installed content hashes
```

- `segment_offsets` is the interesting field: for byte-range playback you precompute a table of `(offset, length)` per rendition so the player (or the appliance) can seek with one range request and know where the keyframes are.
- `housekeeping` is the Netflix-unique table: which appliance in which ISP has which title, and how full each appliance is. Fill management is an operational database problem.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Control plane (low QPS, authoritative)
  Catalog -> Transcode Pipeline -> Asset Manifest DB (title -> renditions -> offsets)
  Licensing/DRM service (per-profile keys, heartbeats, revocation)
  Recommendation (offline jobs -> materialized candidate lists)

Playback data plane (all the QPS)
  App (TV/phone) -> DNS/Anycast -> Open Connect appliance in ISP network
        if hit: serve from local disk / SSD  (~99% of streams)
        if miss: regional Netflix cache (DGX nodes) -> origin storage
  |
  +--> Player: DASH/HLS, ABR, byte-range seeking, 10s heartbeat

Supporting
  Search service, My List / profiles service, playback events -> Kafka -> QoE + viewing analytics
```

Two things to diagram clearly: (a) the control plane vs playback data plane split, and (b) that the Open Connect appliance sits in the ISP, so the ISP *is* the edge and Netflix pays for the hardware.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Open Connect: moving the cost off the price sheet**

- Netflix deploys **Open Connect appliances (NGD, later NGD+)** directly into ISP networks. Traffic from a subscriber in ISP X hits appliance X with no internet transit and no peering.
- The economics: transit/peering costs money per GB; hardware is a **capital expense amortized over ~5 years**. At 12 PB/day, transit at even $0.01/GB is $120k/day. Owning the last mile converts that into a hardware line item.
- Consequences on the engineering side: appliances are managed through a fleet control plane; fill is driven by viewing data with regional skew (Latin America and India need different fills than the US); titles are immutable so fill is a file copy, not a sync; appliance storage is provisioned per ISP with headroom; and **not every ISP accepts appliances**, so those regions use a commercial CDN.
- Design implication for interviews: the "CDN" is not a vendor, it is part of your system. You need an appliance fleet, an inventory system, a fill pipeline, and a failover story (what does the player do when an appliance is down? → fall back to the regional cache/DNS, with a different latency profile).
- The honest limitation: this only works because the catalog is small relative to reach (450 TB into 40 PB deployed). Netflix has said larger catalogs and live content are the pressure points, and live pushes traffic back onto the internet transit.

**7.2 Byte-range playback vs segmented streaming (Netflix's VOD choice)**

- Netflix's VOD choice is **byte-range requests into a single large MP4** (or DASH on a range-capable file), with precomputed offset tables. Streaming delivery to a TV over a controlled set of CDNs has predictable throughput, so segmentation buys nothing, while seeking on a single file is one request with an exact range.
- YouTube uses segmented HLS/fMP4 because its network is the open internet with wildly variable throughput, where chunked delivery lets the player re-evaluate.
- Trade-offs to state: byte-range is simpler and seeks cleanly, but wastes the `moov` atom fetch and has no per-chunk failover; segmented is more robust to variable networks and gives easy per-chunk CDN caching, but a segment can be cached as a separate object (memory and eviction pressure) and seeking needs an index.
- Netflix mitigates the "download to find moov" issue by fast-starting the `moov` atom for range-enabled files.

**7.3 QoE at TV scale and the constrained-device problem**

- TVs are the worst client: weaker CPUs, worse Wi-Fi, more latency in their own stack, and often aggressive power-saving that throttles decode. So the QoE targets are tighter (rebuffer < 0.25%) and you cannot rely on the client to hide problems.
- Player-side engineering that matters: pre-warm the connection and DNS, buffer ~30 s of startup margin on a Wi-Fi-class network, cap the number of ABResolves (each costs a round trip), and use a **fixed ladder on TVs** rather than adaptive, because TVs often make bad bandwidth estimates and a stable lower rendition is better than an oscillating one.
- Adaptive logic specifics: buffer-aware switching (only upgrade when buffer > 2× target), hysteresis to prevent oscillation, and a hard cap on downward switches per session to avoid a death spiral.
- Telemetry: the 10-second heartbeat carries position, selected bitrate, buffer level, rebuffer events, and error codes — enough to compute QoE per session, per ISP, per device class, and per app version. **Segmenting by ISP is the key cut**, because an ISP is where you cannot change the last mile and must fix it with fill and appliances.
- Also: audio-only for background playback, and a 240p/360p floor so a bad connection degrades to watchable rather than buffering.

**7.4 Live: the part that breaks the model**

- A small fraction of Netflix viewing is live (sports, provider channels). Live cannot use Open Connect appliances, because live is not a fillable file — it is a continuous origin problem behind internet transit.
- Live path: provider feed to ingest to transcoder to packager to a commercial CDN or Netflix's own live distribution, with 2–4 s segments and short TTLs. Cost per GB is several times higher than VOD.
- What breaks: peak concurrency for a big event (a championship serves tens of millions of concurrent streams, 50–100× a normal evening), and the CDN fill is irrelevant so you are fully exposed to transit cost. Netflix mitigates with capacity reservations, per-region event planning, and caching the DVR/replay window through Open Connect *after* the live event, which converts live egress into VOD egress for the majority of viewers who watch the replay.

**7.5 Downloads, licensing, and the offline path**

- Downloads use a **separate, lower-bitrate rendition ladder** (usually up to 720p) because the file must live on a phone, plus a persistent FairPlay/Widevine offline license tied to the device and account.
- The license is time-limited and revocable; Netflix enforces a download expiry so offline content disappears.
- This creates a distinct key hierarchy: content key → per-title key → per-profile rental key → per-device offline license. Getting this wrong is a piracy incident, so the key service is isolated and audited.
- Bandwidth: a 2-hour 720p download at ~1.5 Mbps is **1.35 GB**, which is a large fraction of a mobile plan — so downloads are explicitly user-initiated, resumable, and only on Wi-Fi by default.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **Appliance fill bandwidth and cost** — shipping terabytes per week into hundreds of ISP networks; a bottleneck at a single large ISP delays fills everywhere.
2. **Appliance fleet management** — hundreds of thousands of deployed devices; the control plane, config pushes, and health monitoring are themselves a distributed system.
3. **Live event peaks** — 50–100× normal concurrency, entirely on transit-priced bandwidth.
4. **CDN fill for the long tail** — titles nobody in a region watches consume appliance disk for nothing; fill must be demand-driven with a storage cap per appliance.
5. **The catalog growing** — the 90× replication model degrades as the catalog grows relative to PoP count.
6. **DRM/license QPS** at peak (200M concurrent streams × a heartbeat every 10 s = **20M license heartbeats/sec** at peak) — this is the number people miss and it forces the license service to be huge and shardable, or the heartbeat interval to be adaptive.
7. **Start latency on TV** — client-side, but any server metadata latency shows up as TTFF.
8. **Search and browse** — browse rows are cached per locale but browsing is a large fraction of sessions.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Open Connect appliance down | player falls back to the regional cache/DNS, slower start; **no playback outage** | redundant appliances per ISP, health-driven DNS |
| Appliance disk corrupt/truncated | per-title errors on that appliance | periodic content hash verification against `housekeeping` inventory |
| Regional cache miss storm | origin (object storage) takes the hit | shield caches, hot-title prefill |
| Origin storage issue | titles unavailable in that region | multi-region replication; manifest returns 403 |
| DRM license service down | **playback blocks** (fail closed) | this is the one hard dependency; must be highly available, degrade to a longer lease window rather than open access |
| Transcode pipeline down | new titles unavailable; existing unaffected | queue + ETA in catalog |
| Live event overruns capacity | rebuffer for a subset | pre-provisioned capacity; admission limits; DVR fallback |
| Player bug on a device model | stalls on a device class | feature-flag by device, canary by app version |
| Metadata API slow | player waits | CDN-cached manifest; heartbeat made optional under load (documented trade) |
| ISP appliance fill stale | title missing on that ISP | fall back to regional CDN until filled |
| Heartbeat revocation missed | playback continues up to one interval | 10 s interval is the bound; accept it |
| Catalog bug (wrong availability window) | title served early/late | manifest is authoritative and re-cached in seconds |

Invariant: **playback of licensed content degrades in quality and start latency long before it degrades in availability.** That is a deliberate design property (progressive ladder, heartbeat leases, appliance→CDN fallback).

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Last mile | owned Open Connect appliances | commercial CDN | transit cost per GB dominates; hardware is cheaper over 5 years |
| VOD packaging | byte-range into single files | segmented streaming | controlled network, predictable throughput, exact seeking |
| Ladder | adaptive on mobile/web, **fixed on TV** | adaptive everywhere | TVs estimate bandwidth badly; oscillation is worse than a stable lower rung |
| Audio | separate track, multi-language | baked-in audio | 10 languages otherwise |
| DRM | real DRM with offline licenses | watermarking | watermarking deters, does not prevent |
| Availability window | manifest with heartbeat revocation | long-lived permissive manifest | revocation within ~10 s |
| Ranking | offline materialized candidates | request-time ranking | same reasons as Twitter; plus it is a business-critical model |
| Fill strategy | demand-driven with per-appliance cap | mirror everything | the long tail would never fit |
| Live distribution | transit-priced CDN, DVR replay via Open Connect | fill live | live is not a file |
| Profiles | per-profile playback policy and keys | per-account only | shared accounts are the norm; parental controls need it |

Rejected wholesale
- **Relying entirely on a commercial CDN for VOD.** At 12 PB/day that is a seven-figure monthly line item with a negotiating position of one.
- **Segmented HLS for all VOD on controlled networks.** Extra requests and cache entries for no benefit.
- **Open Connect everywhere.** Large ISPs may refuse, and some geographies are served by partners; the design needs both paths from day one.
- **Uncached metadata per playback.** 200M concurrent starts against a metadata database is not a design, it is an outage.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "Why does Netflix put hardware in ISP networks? Why not just use Akamai?"**
Model answer: economics. At roughly 12 PB/day of VOD egress, even a heavily negotiated commercial CDN rate becomes a nine-figure annual line item, and the ISP's own transit is what you are avoiding. An Open Connect appliance is a capital expense amortized over about five years with essentially no per-GB cost, and it removes transit *and* peering *and* the last-mile capacity problem, because the appliance is inside the network. It also improves TTFF, since the player hits local disk instead of a peering path. What I would not claim is that it is universally better: it only works because the catalog is small relative to reach — about 450 TB working set against 40 PB deployed, a roughly 90× replication factor. As the catalog grows that ratio degrades, and live content cannot use appliances at all because a live stream is not a fillable file. And some ISPs will not take hardware, so a commercial CDN path has to exist regardless. So the real design is a two-tier CDN: owned appliances where they exist, commercial CDN everywhere else, with the player unaware of the difference.

**Q2. "Walk me through exactly what happens when a user presses play."**
Model answer: The app has already cached the catalog row for this title (browse and search pre-warm it), so play is mostly local. It requests the manifest with its profile and device capability; if that row is not cached, one catalog call returns the renditions, audio tracks, subtitles, DRM header, byte-range offset tables, and the heartbeat interval. The app then picks a starting rendition from the previous session's bandwidth estimate, not from the raw download speed, and issues a range request into the Open Connect appliance that DNS resolves to for that ISP. The appliance serves from local SSD; on a miss it pulls from the regional cache, and only then from origin, and that miss rate is the number the whole fill operation exists to minimize. First frame renders after one range request, usually under a second. From then on every 10 seconds the player sends a heartbeat with position, selected bitrate, buffer level, and rebuffer events; that is both the DRM lease renewal and the QoE telemetry. ABR switches happen in-buffer with hysteresis, so no request for a new manifest is needed unless the device changes or the lease needs refreshing.

**Q3. "How do you keep playback under 2 seconds on a TV?"**
Model answer: TVs are the hardest client, so the work is split. Server side: manifests and asset metadata are cached at the CDN with the appliance serving bytes from local disk, so there is no origin in the path; the manifest response is small and pre-computed so there is nothing to assemble per request. Client side, which is where most of the win is: pre-warm DNS and TLS at app launch so handshake cost is not on the play path; on a TV-class network, start with a conservative rendition and a 30-second buffer margin rather than optimistically going high; keep the ladder fixed on TVs, because TV Wi-Fi and TV bandwidth estimators are both bad and an oscillating bitrate is worse than a stable lower one; and cap ABR probes per session because each probe is a round trip the TV pays for. Then measure by device class: TTFF and rebuffer segmented by device model and app version, because the top offenders are always a specific firmware revision. If TTFF regresses after a client release it is always the client, so I would put the canary and the device-level dashboard ahead of any CDN investigation.

**Q4. "How does your design change for live content?"**
Model answer: It leaves Open Connect entirely. Live is a continuous origin problem behind the internet: provider feed to regional ingest to a low-latency transcoder to a packager emitting 2–4 second segments with short TTLs, delivered over commercial CDN or our own transit, at per-GB rates several times higher than VOD. The consequences are structural. First, cost: a championship serves tens of millions of concurrent streams, 50–100× a normal evening, all priced at live rates, and the appliance model cannot help. Second, we plan capacity per event with reservations rather than autoscaling, because there is no time to spin up. Third, replay is where Open Connect comes back: after the event, the DVR window is transcoded to files and filled to the appliances, so the majority of viewers who watch later consume it as ordinary VOD. That single move converts the most expensive bytes in the system into the cheapest, and it is the main reason live economics are survivable. What breaks first is peak concurrency and then transit cost; the mitigations are capacity reservations, admission control per region, and being explicit that live is a lower-margin business than VOD.

**Q5. "A title is pulled from the catalog. How fast does it stop playing?"**
Model answer: Two mechanisms with different speeds, and I will be honest about both. The fast one is the heartbeat: the manifest tells the client to re-check every 10 seconds, so the licensing service can revoke a title and every playing session stops within roughly that interval, without any CDN purge. That covers the "still playing right now" case. The slower one is byte-range URLs, which are immutable and content-addressed — so any client that already has a URL can keep fetching for as long as it holds them, and a CDN purge only helps at the next request. That is the direct cost of immutability, and I accepted it because purging a single object across hundreds of PoPs is slow anyway and because with a heartbeat I get a better bound than purge timing. For a licensed title I would also shorten the effective lease rather than rely on purge. And I keep an immutable audit record of the revocation with actor and timestamp, because "why did this title disappear" is a question that gets asked repeatedly and an unanswerable takedown is its own problem.

**Q6. "How do you decide how much content to put on each appliance?"**
Model answer: Demand-driven fill with a hard per-appliance storage cap, because the long tail will happily fill every disk in the network with content nobody plays. The pipeline watches viewing share per title per ISP, and a title gets filled to an ISP when its local share exceeds a threshold — something like top-1000 titles per region plus anything trending locally or newly released. Appliances report their inventory and free space back to a housekeeping system, so fill decisions are made against actual capacity, not planned capacity. Newly released titles are filled everywhere with priority because of launch concentration, and pre-positioning is scheduled backwards from the release date. Everything is immutable, so fill is a file copy with hash verification rather than a synchronization protocol, which is what makes a 40 PB deployed footprint operationally possible. The residual: because fill lags release by hours, a global launch day has a real cold-start window, and the mitigation is a commercial CDN in front of the appliances for exactly that period — which is how the two-tier design earns its keep.

---

## 12.5.10 Dropbox / Google Drive

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design Dropbox. Users sync folders across devices, files can be terabytes, and edits can happen on several devices at once. Build the sync engine."

The interviewer is scoring: **sync, not storage** — the hard part is change detection, conflict resolution, and delta sync over a flaky consumer network, not object storage. Secondary: dedup, snapshot history, sharing with permissions, and why the client holds a local index and does most of the work.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Upload/download/delete/rename/move across devices; folder sync.
- F2. Conflict detection and resolution (the hard part).
- F3. Offline changes queue and replay on reconnect.
- F4. Content-hash dedup with cross-file and cross-user dedup.
- F5. Snapshot history / version restore.
- F6. Sharing: link shares with permissions (view/comment/edit), team folders.
- F7. Selective sync / files-on-demand (hydrate on open, not on sync).
- F8. Paper, scanning, and offline pin of key files.

Non-functional
- N1. Sync latency: a change on device A visible on device B **< 5 s** when online.
- N2. Must work on 3G and on metered connections; **delta-only transfer** is mandatory.
- N3. Bandwidth efficiency: re-syncing an unchanged 2 GB folder costs ~0 bytes.
- N4. File size up to **5 TB** (Dropbox's cap is tens of GB per file; Drive allows large uploads via resumable chunks).
- N5. Durability RPO = 0 from the user's perspective once "saved" is shown.
- N6. Availability 99.9% for the sync API; **the client must keep working offline for days**.
- N7. Security: client-side encryption at rest is a plus; per-file keys; sharing links revocable.

---

**3. Estimation**

Users and volume
- 700M users × 400 files × 2 MB average = **560B files, 560 TB of unique data** (before dedup and multi-version).
- Delta history: each file keeps ~10 versions → multi-petabyte object store, but **1.5 PB at most** with lifecycle.
- Metadata: 560B files × 500 B = **280 TB** of metadata. This is the number that surprises people — metadata dwarfs a normal workload and needs its own sharded store, not a single relational table.

Upload/write volume
- 700M users × 20 changes/day = 14B changes/day = **162k changes/sec average**, 650k/sec peak.
- Bytes: 14M real uploads/day × 2 MB = **28 TB/day** ingest.

Read/download volume
- 700M × 30 file opens/day = 21B opens/day = **243k/sec**; download bytes ~120 TB/day.

Bandwidth
- This is the real constraint: **egress dominates**. 120 TB/day × $0.02 = $2.4k/day. But the sync *polling* cost is worse: naive polling every 30 s across 700M devices is 23M requests/sec doing nothing. Fix: **long-poll / push channel**, which is why the design below is push-based with a delta endpoint.

Dedupe arithmetic
- With content hashing, dedup rate on real user data is 20–40%. 560 TB becomes **~380 TB unique**. Savings: 180 TB × $0.023/GB-month ≈ **$4,100/month**. Real, and worth stating; also it makes re-uploads of the same file free.

Arithmetic to state
- "560B files at 500 B of metadata is 280 TB. At 162k writes/sec you cannot put metadata in one Postgres — you shard by a hash of the file's parent folder so all children of a folder are co-located, which is what makes folder listings and recursive syncs cheap."

---

**4. API design**

Sync bootstrap (per device)
```
POST /sync/v2/bootstrap   { "cursor": "...", "root": "/Photos" }
-> { "root": {...}, "entries": [...], "cursor": "c_8812", "hasMore": false,
     "serverTime": 1767000000, "quotaBytes": {...} }
```

Change feed (the heart of it)
```
GET /sync/v2/changes?cursor=c_8812&limit=1000&path=/Photos
-> { "changes": [
       { "op": "upsert", "path": "/Photos/a.jpg", "rev": "r_9f3a", "contentHash": "h_1a2b",
         "size": 2097152, "mtime": 1767000000, "serverModTime": 1767000001,
         "sharing": {...}, "isFolder": false, "previewUrl": "..." },
       { "op": "delete", "path": "/Photos/old.jpg", "rev": "r_1b2c" },
       { "op": "rename", "from": "/a.jpg", "to": "/b.jpg" } ],
     "cursor": "c_9001", "hasMore": true, "serverTime": 1767000004 }
```
`GET /sync/v2/changes?long_poll=1&timeout=25000` → long-poll so idle devices cost one parked connection, not 2 requests/sec. 700M parked long-polls is a large number of sockets — which is why the design keeps metadata rows in a **distributed cache with long-lived subscriptions** rather than per-client DB connections.

Delta transfer
```
POST /sync/v2/upload/session  { "path": "/a/big.iso", "size": 3221225472, "contentHash": "h_77" }
-> { "sessionId": "...", "offset": 0, "receivedBytes": 0 }   // resumable, server tells you the offset
PUT  /sync/v2/upload/{sessionId}?offset=4194304   (raw bytes, Content-Range style)
-> 308 / { "receivedBytes": 8388608 }
POST /sync/v2/upload/{sessionId}/commit  { "contentHash": "h_77" }
-> { "rev": "r_new", "deduplicated": true }
```
Content-addressed commit: the server hashes what it received and, if `h_77` already exists, stores **zero new bytes** and returns `deduplicated: true`. That is why the client must hash before uploading — the hash is the dedup key.

Revisions and sharing
```
GET  /sync/v2/file/{path}?rev=r_9f3a          -> metadata + preview + download link
GET  /files/{path}?dl=1                       -> content (or a signed CDN URL)
GET  /sync/v2/revisions/{path}                -> list of revisions with authors and times
POST /sync/v2/revisions/{path}/restore        -> creates a new revision from an old one
POST /sharing/link  { path, permission, expiry, password }
PUT  /sharing/link/{id}/revoke
```
Errors
| Case | Code | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| Cursor too old | 410 `cursor_expired` | client does a full re-index; **this is a normal, expected path, not a bug** |
| Quota exceeded | 507 | per-user storage cap |
| File too large | 413 | |
| Path conflict | 409 with both revisions | **expected**; client resolves per policy |
| Download link expired | 403 | re-request a signed URL |
| Rate limit | 429 | client backs off with jitter |
| Folder deleted while syncing | 410 on path | client removes locally after reporting |

---

**5. Data model**

Two-tier, and the split is the design.
```
Content-addressed blob store
  blob: h_1a2b3c   -> immutable object bytes (S3/GCS), never mutated, never deleted
  refcount         -> number of file nodes pointing at this hash
```
```
Nodes (mutable metadata), sharded by hash(parent_folder_id) % N
  file_node
    node_id        UUID PRIMARY KEY
    parent_id      UUID
    name           TEXT
    content_hash   TEXT         -- reference to a blob
    size, mtime, server_mod_time
    rev            TEXT
    deleted        BOOL
    owner_id       UUID
    sharing_state  JSONB       -- inherit / explicit / link
    version        INT
    INDEX (parent_id, name)
    INDEX (parent_id, server_mod_time)  -- for folder change feeds
```
```
Revision history
  (node_id, rev, content_hash, author_id, created_at, source_device)
```
```
Devices and sync cursors
  device_id, user_id, last_seen, cursor, platform, app_version
  INDEX (user_id)
```
```
Client state (never server state, and this is the point)
  local index: path -> (rev, content_hash, mtime, size)
  pending changes queue, with per-change conflict resolution outcome
```

Content-hash detail: use **SHA-256 of file content for leaf dedup** and a **Merkle tree over sorted (name, content_hash) pairs for folders**. Folder hashing is what lets a client ask "has this subtree changed?" in O(1) — it hashes its local folder the same way and compares. That is how you get "re-sync an unchanged 2 GB folder costs zero bytes": the client compares one folder hash, learns nothing changed, and stops.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Device A (Dropbox client)
   local index + local blob cache + pending change queue
   |
   | 1. on startup / on long-poll wakeup:
   |    GET /sync/v2/changes?cursor=...      -> ordered op list
   | 2. apply ops to local index
   | 3. compute local folder Merkle hashes, compare with server -> find real diffs
   | 4. for changed files: hash -> compare content_hash -> download only if absent
   |    (files-on-demand: download happens on open, not on sync)
   | 5. upload queue: hash first -> POST upload session (resumable) -> commit
   | 6. conflicts: both-revoked -> local copy + server copy + user choice
   v
Sync Service (stateless, per-shard, metadata hot in cache)
   +--> Metadata store (sharded by parent_id) - strong per-folder consistency
   +--> Blob store (S3, immutable, content-addressed, refcounted)
   +--> Notification bus (Kafka/pub-sub) -> per-device long-poll connections
   +--> Versioning + snapshot service
   +--> Sharing / permission service
   +--> Thumbnail + preview renderer (async)
```

Read path for a file open: app → metadata lookup → signed CDN URL → range read from blob store. One round trip plus the blob fetch; the sync path is entirely independent.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Conflict detection and resolution: the actual algorithm**

Three cases, and the detection method matters more than the resolution policy.
- **Detection**: every mutation creates a new `rev` with `server_mod_time` and an `author_device`. A client tracks the `rev` it last saw per file. If the server's current `rev` differs from the client's base `rev` *and* the client has a pending local change to that file, that is a conflict. Content-hash equality short-circuits it: if both sides produced the same hash, there is no conflict, just two paths to the same state.
- **Case 1: file created independently on two devices with different content.** Both write; the server keeps both revisions and links them as sibling versions. The client shows "Two versions of this file" and the user picks — with the loser saved as `<name> (device's copy)`. Dropbox's actual behavior.
- **Case 2: delete vs edit.** Hard-delete wins by default but the losing revision is retained for 30 days, so the user's work is never silently destroyed. Prefer "the edit survives as a revision."
- **Case 3: concurrent edits to the same file.** Resolution depends on the file type:
  - **Text and small files (< 10 MB)**: three-way merge. The client has base (last-synced rev), local, and remote, and can run a real diff3 merge. Dropbox does not merge by default but surfaces a "keep both" option; offering merge for text is a defensible product decision.
  - **Binary or large files**: never attempt a merge; produce both versions.
- **The rule to state**: "conflict is not an error path, it is a first-class outcome, and the system must guarantee that no user content is ever lost — every losing version is preserved as a revision." Then add: server-side file locking (Dropbox has this) plus "file is open by X" notifications for the document case, because for actively-edited documents automatic merging is the wrong tool and lock semantics is the right one.
- **The pathological case**: an editor that writes the file every keystroke. Fix: the client debounces (write temp + rename, coalesce on a 2 s timer) so only meaningful checkpoints become revisions. Never put 3,000 revisions in the history of a file the user typed for a minute.

**7.2 Delta sync over a bad network**

The requirement is that a no-change sync costs ~0 bytes. Three mechanisms:
1. **Long-poll change feed.** Instead of polling, the client parks on `/changes?long_poll=1&timeout=25000`; the server holds the connection and returns when there is work or on timeout. 700M parked connections is the cost, and it is much cheaper than 23M requests/sec. Fan out the wakeups from a Kafka topic per user, not from the database.
2. **Folder Merkle hashing.** Sort children by (name, content_hash), hash the list recursively. The client computes the same hash for its local tree and sends only the differing folder hashes plus paths. So a changed subfolder deep in a tree costs one path, not a recursive listing. This is the single highest-leverage idea in the whole sync engine.
3. **Content-hash comparison before download.** The client already has the file locally; it compares the hash in the change record with its local index and skips the transfer. Combined with dedup on the server, a re-upload of the same file costs zero bytes.
Also: **resumable uploads with server-reported offset**, so a dropped connection on a 5 GB file resumes from the last acknowledged offset rather than restarting. And on mobile, "files on demand" — sync metadata only, download on open — which turns 700M devices from a bandwidth problem into a metadata problem.

**7.3 Dedupe, refcounting, and the delete race**

- Dedup key = content hash (SHA-256). Server checks existence on commit; if present, store nothing and return `deduplicated: true`.
- Dedup must be **global** (across users) for the savings to materialize, which is also the biggest privacy and abuse surface — an attacker who can guess a hash can confirm whether a file exists. Mitigations: do not expose existence for other users' content, use a keyed hash (HMAC with a per-tenant secret) so hashes are not comparable across tenants, and rate-limit hash-probe endpoints.
- **Refcounting** is where delete bugs live. Two writers decrementing the same blob can drop the count to zero and delete bytes another user's revision points at. Design: refcount is derived, not trusted — a nightly reconciliation recomputes refcounts from the nodes table and repairs any blob whose count is wrong or whose last referrer vanished. Deletes are tombstoned and garbage-collected after a delay (30 days) with a "recently deleted" recovery window, so a premature GC is recoverable. And **never decrement on a soft delete** — only on hard delete after the retention window.
- Also worth naming: hashing cost. SHA-256 at ~500 MB/s/core, so a 2 GB file costs 4 CPU-seconds to hash on the client (parallelizable) and again on the server. For huge files, use a **tree hash** (Merkle over fixed-size blocks) so the server can verify blocks incrementally and the client can resume by block.

**7.4 Snapshot history and the cost of "never lose data"**

- Every mutation is a revision `(node_id, rev, content_hash, author, time)`. Because blobs are immutable and deduped, a revision is **~100 bytes of metadata plus a reference** — history is cheap, which is why you can keep 100 versions of every file.
- Periodic **snapshots** aggregate revisions into a named restore point (per day per folder) so restore is one operation rather than a replay.
- The rules that make it affordable: never store a new blob for an unchanged file (dedup does this); expire revisions older than the user's history window (30–180 days) but keep the *current* blob forever; and expire the retained version's blob only when no node or revision in its retention window references it (GC driven by the nodes+revisions tables, not by the blob store).
- Trash: a per-user trash that is a soft delete with a 30-day retention, and the "restore" path must be able to reconstruct the exact path hierarchy, which means deleted folders keep their subtree of nodes in a `deleted_root` state rather than being erased.
- This is the section where "metadata dwarfs content" shows up again: 560B nodes × ~20 revisions retained is an enormous metadata table, which is exactly why nodes are sharded by parent and history is in a separate columnar-ish store.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **Long-poll connection count.** 700M parked connections at 25 s timeout. Fixes: multiplex many users' notifications per node, share the park across users via Kafka fan-out, and increase the timeout where carriers or NATs permit. A leaked connection is a memory leak, so idle timeouts must be enforced server-side too.
2. **The change feed's per-folder hot spot.** A folder that 50,000 people share (a team folder) makes `parent_id`-partitioned metadata a single hot shard. Fix: shard hot folders by a different key, or make the change feed derived from a Kafka log rather than read from the metadata store.
3. **Egress for downloads**, and metered/mobile egress. Fix: CDN for content, and files-on-demand to reduce total download volume.
4. **The metadata store** at 280 TB and 162k writes/sec. Fix: shard by `hash(parent_id)`, which gives both write distribution and cheap recursive listing; keep the hot path in a distributed cache with change-broadcast invalidation.
5. **Blob GC and refcount reconciliation** cost — full-table reconciliation on 560B nodes must be incremental and shard-parallel.
6. **Thumbnail/preview rendering** for millions of new files/day (image/video/doc converters are CPU-hungry and failure-prone; treat as a separate async tier with per-type queues).
7. **Shared-folder ACL complexity** — permission checks per file open on a hierarchy with inheritance is a graph traversal; cache resolved permissions per (user, folder) with invalidation on ACL change.
8. **Abuse**: copyrighted content, malware distribution in zips, and public-link abuse. Needs scan-on-ingest and link revocation.
9. **The initial bootstrap** for a new device — millions of files to enumerate. Fix: lazy folder enumeration driven by what the user opens, plus a "full sync" mode that is explicitly opt-in for very large accounts.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Metadata store shard down | that folder subtree is unavailable; other folders work | replica promotion; sync retries with backoff, cursor preserved |
| Cursor expired (server pruned history) | 410 → client does a **full re-index** from local hashes | this is a designed path, not an outage; keep cursors valid for 30+ days |
| Blob store down | metadata works, downloads/uploads fail; the UI must distinguish "indexed" from "downloaded" | retry with resumable offset |
| Notification bus down | clients keep long-polling and time out; sync degrades to polling on interval | restart consumers; log replay |
| Upload interrupted | resumable from the last acknowledged offset | per-session offset is authoritative, so partial uploads are harmless |
| Conflict storm (a mass rename) | thousands of changes per second per folder | rate-limit and batch the change feed; rename is one op in the feed, not N |
| GC deletes a live blob | download 404 | reconciliation repairs; keep a delayed tombstone window |
| Preview/thumbnail service down | files sync fine; previews missing | async retry; do not block sync on previews |
| Permission service down | share links and team ACLs unavailable; personal sync continues | fail closed for shared content, fail open for personal |
| Client disk full | device shows "paused syncing" and queues locally | explicit UI; never delete unsynced local changes silently |
| Hash mismatch on upload | server rejects the commit; client re-chunks and retries | indicates corruption; alert if repeated |
| Region outage | nodes and blobs are multi-region replicated; cursors are per-user not per-region | route to the healthy region |

The invariant worth stating: **local state is authoritative for what the user has; the server is authoritative for ordering and durability; the change feed is the only contract between them.** Everything else can be recomputed.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Change feed | push/long-poll | polling every N seconds | 23M req/sec doing nothing vs 700M parked sockets |
| Change detection | folder Merkle hash + content hash | listing the whole tree | a no-change sync costs one hash |
| Upload dedup | client hashes first, server commits by hash | upload then hash server-side | saves all bandwidth for re-uploads |
| Conflicts | keep both + preserve every revision | last-write-wins | last-write-wins silently destroys user work |
| Text merge | optional three-way merge | automatic merge always | binary merge is data loss |
| History | revisions as references to immutable blobs | copying files per version | history becomes nearly free |
| Delete | soft delete + 30-day trash + delayed GC | immediate blob delete | the GC/refcount race is unavoidable; delay makes it recoverable |
| Per-file size | chunked, resumable, tree-hashed | single PUT | 5 TB files and mobile networks make a single PUT unusable |
| Fan-out for large folders | folder-hash diff | per-file compare | a 1M-file folder must not cost 1M comparisons |
| Sync priority | interactive-first, then bulk | FIFO | a small edit must not queue behind a 500 GB backup |
| Encryption | server-side at rest + optional client-side (zero-knowledge) | plaintext blobs | e2ee makes search/preview a different design entirely |
| Shared-folder hot shard | shard by alternate key for hot folders | one hash | a 50k-member team folder is a single-shard hotspot |

Rejected wholesale
- **A replicated filesystem (NFS/FUSE) instead of a sync engine.** Consumers do not have reliable, low-latency, always-on connectivity, and Nagle's law plus intermittent networks make it unworkable. Sync, not mount.
- **Storing the file tree as one giant JSON blob per folder.** Listing a 1M-file folder becomes a 200 MB response.
- **Object storage with the directory as the key.** Object stores have no cheap rename, and rename is the most common sync operation.
- **Deleting content immediately on delete.** A GC race destroys another user's data, and there is no recovery.
- **Rewriting the whole file on every save (no content hashing).** Bandwidth and cost blow up immediately on a large file.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "Walk me through syncing a folder with two devices that both changed the same file."**
Model answer: Device A and device B both hold `r_7` as their base. Both edit and both upload. The server serializes: whichever commit lands first creates `r_8`, the second commit is rejected with 409 and both content hashes are returned. Device B then does not silently overwrite; it uploads its version as a sibling revision, and the sync feed tells A that a new sibling exists. So the user sees two versions of the file — the server keeps both, plus the revision history, so nothing is lost. Detection is not "who wrote last": it is "does my base rev differ from the server's current rev while I have a pending local change." If both devices happened to produce identical content, the content hashes match, the commit deduplicates, and there is no conflict at all — the hash comparison short-circuits the entire case. For a text file I would offer a three-way merge, since the client has base, local, and remote and diff3 is straightforward under 10 MB. For binary or huge files I never merge; two versions and a choice. And if the file is currently open in a collaborative editor, merging is wrong entirely, so file locking plus "X is editing this" is the right semantic — the lock and the merge strategy are different tools for different file classes.

**Q2. "Why do you need long polling? Why not have the client poll every 30 seconds?"**
Model answer: Arithmetic. 700M devices polling every 30 seconds is 23 million requests per second, almost all returning an empty response, which costs more than the entire metadata and content infrastructure combined. Long polling parks each client on a connection that the server holds for 25 seconds and returns immediately when there is work, so idle cost is one parked connection per device and each change costs one wakeup. The cost of that choice is connection count — 700M parked sockets — which is real: a leaked connection is a memory leak, so idle enforcement has to be server-side, and each gateway multiplexes many users' notifications from a Kafka topic rather than holding a database connection per client. A middle option is Server-Sent Events or WebSockets with a fallback to a longer poll interval, which is what mobile clients do when they are in the background. The change feed payload is the same in all cases: an ordered op list with a cursor, so a client that misses a wakeup entirely still catches up by polling with its cursor.

**Q3. "A user has a 500 GB folder and hasn't changed anything. What does sync cost?"**
Model answer: Effectively zero, and that is by design rather than by luck. On reconnect the client pulls its change feed with its cursor and gets nothing. Then it computes the Merkle hash of each local folder — sort children by name and content hash, hash the list, recurse — and sends only the folder hashes. The server compares and finds every subtree identical, so it returns no file paths at all. The cost is a few kilobytes of hashes and one round trip, not 500 GB of listing or comparison. If nothing changed, sync is complete. When one file changes deep in the tree, only that file's path is returned, not the subtree. That property is why the design can support 700M devices: without folder hashing, a 500 GB account would either re-list the whole tree on every check or never notice a change. I should also mention the escape hatch, because it is the honest failure mode: Merkle hashing the entire tree is itself O(tree), so for very large accounts the client compares lazily — it only descends into folders the user actually opens or that the server flags, and files-on-demand means most files are never hashed at all until they are needed.

**Q4. "How do you handle a 4 TB file on a flaky connection?"**
Model answer: Tree-hashed, chunked, resumable, and never held in memory. The file is split into fixed-size blocks, each block hashed into a Merkle tree, and the client hashes blocks incrementally so uploading does not require a second pass. Upload is a session: I create a session with the declared size and root hash, then PUT blocks with explicit offsets and a `PUT` of the session's current received offset so the client resumes from exactly where the server stopped. Interruption costs one block, not one file. The server streams to temporary storage, verifies the tree hash on commit, and then either stores the object or, if the hash already exists, discards the bytes and reports deduplicated — so a repeated upload of a large file costs bandwidth and nothing else. On read, byte ranges come straight from the blob store through a CDN, so a partial download works and seeking is a range request. The 4 TB number also forces the client to be streaming throughout: never read the whole file to hash it, never hold it in RAM, and write to a temp file then rename, so a crash leaves an obvious partial artifact rather than a corrupt file with a valid name.

**Q5. "How does deduplication interact with deletion, and with privacy?"**
Model answer: Deletion is where dedup systems lose data. Because one blob can back many nodes across many users, a naive refcount decrement from two concurrent deletes can reach zero while another user's revision still points at it, and then those bytes are gone. So refcount is treated as derived, not trusted: a nightly incremental reconciliation recomputes counts from the nodes and revisions tables and repairs any blob whose count is wrong, and the actual delete is a tombstone plus a delayed GC rather than an immediate unlink. Soft deletes never decrement at all, which is why the trash window keeps working. On privacy, global dedup is a real information leak: if the hash is guessable, I can confirm whether a given file exists in the system, and worse, learn that two users share content. So the dedup key is an HMAC over the content hash with a per-tenant secret, making hashes non-comparable across tenants while still deduplicating within a tenant; hash-probe endpoints are rate-limited; and existence of another user's content is never revealed. That is the trade: global dedup saves 30% of storage, and tenant-scoped dedup with keyed hashes costs some of that back in exchange for not being an oracle.

**Q6. "How would you add real-time collaborative editing to this?"**
Model answer: Do not put it in the sync engine — that is the actual answer. Sync is a whole-file, last-writer-wins-with-preservation model; collaborative editing is a CRDT or OT log with character-level operations. Adding them together produces a system that is bad at both. So I would treat it as a separate service with its own data model: the file has an associated `doc` identity, the editor clients maintain an operation log against a server-ordered sequence, the server broadcasts ops over the existing long-poll/websocket infrastructure, and periodically snapshots the document into the content-addressed blob store so it becomes a normal revision in the normal version history. Then Dropbox-style syncing still works exactly as before: a device that is not editing receives the merged document as a new revision, and if the same file is edited in two places simultaneously that is the existing conflict case with two revisions. The hard parts are honestly the boring ones: offline editing during a flight needs the op log to be mergeable after a long divergence, which is what CRDTs are for; presence and cursors are ephemeral and must never be persisted; and you need a size threshold above which you refuse to open a file in collaborative mode and fall back to lock-and-sync, because a 4 GB binary in a CRDT is nonsense. The genuinely new infra is: an op-log store, a snapshotter, and a session-presence service. Everything else — permissions, sharing, history, sync — is reused unchanged, which is the payoff of having modeled content as immutable blobs plus mutable nodes from the start.

---

## 12.5.11 Uber (dispatch and matching)

⚫ Expert · **Deep dive**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design Uber's dispatch. Riders and drivers, match them in real time, consider ETA, price, ratings, and surge, and handle surge spikes when demand doubles."

The score is in: this is a **real-time optimization system**, not a CRUD system. Interviewers look for (a) supply/demand modeling and a marketplace with a price mechanism, (b) batch matching vs greedy nearest-driver and when each is better, (c) geospatial indexing and ETA prediction as first-class systems, (d) failure modes at surge, and (e) the driver's location stream — millions of devices writing position at high frequency.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Rider requests a ride; the system assigns a driver.
- F2. Driver location updates, availability toggling, accept/decline, cancel.
- F3. ETA to pickup, ETA to destination, fare estimate, fare calculation.
- F4. Surge pricing multiplier with an explanation and a cap.
- F5. Trip lifecycle: requested → assigned → en route → arrived → in progress → completed → rated.
- F6. Driver earnings, incentives, and payouts.
- F7. Dispatch to a specific driver (rider chooses among candidates) and "accept-later" options.
- F8. Scheduled/advance rides.

Non-functional
- N1. Match latency: **< 5 s** from request to assignment offer at normal load.
- N2. Driver location ingest: **10M updates/sec** (millions of devices × 1/sec).
- N3. Trip state reads: ~100k/sec.
- N4. Global availability; regional isolation is acceptable (a region may degrade independently).
- N5. Fairness and driver-earnings constraints — this is a real product requirement, not a nice-to-have.
- N6. No double assignment; an assignment is committed exactly once.
- N7. ETA accuracy p90 < 3 min pickup ETA at short range.

---

**3. Estimation**

Riders and trips
- 200M monthly riders, 40M rides/day → **463 rides/sec average**, ~3,000/sec peak in a single city, ~25k/sec global peak during events.
- This is a *low-throughput, high-stakes* system, which is the opposite of the feed problems — the real challenge is correctness and latency, not raw scale.

Location stream
- 10M active drivers × 1 update/5 s = **2M updates/sec**, each ~100 B = **200 MB/s ingress**. This is the highest-volume write in the system and it is pure fire-and-forget: **location writes are never transactional with anything.**
- Storage: at 1 update per 20 s retained for 2 hours (for heatmaps and ETAs), 10M drivers × 180 updates = 1.8B points/day = **180 GB/day**. Two hours is the working window; older points go to a columnar lake at 1-minute resolution.

Storage
- Trip row: ~1.5 KB (rider, driver, coordinates, timestamps, fare breakdown, rating). 40M/day × 1.5 KB = **60 GB/day**, 1.8 TB/30 days. Trivial.
- Driver/vehicle/rating: small, relational, fine.

Earnings/ledger
- 40M rides/day × a fare split → a ledger of 40M entries/day, **append-only, immutable, exactly-once**. This is the one place where financial correctness dominates, and it needs double-entry bookkeeping.

Arithmetic to state
- "Location ingest at 2M writes/sec is 400× the trip creation rate. If I put location in the same store as trips, I drown the important data in the unimportant data. So location gets its own sharded, write-optimized path (in-memory grid + periodic flush), and the trip store only sees the matching path."

---

**4. API design**

Rider
```
POST /v1/rides        { "origin": {"lat":37.77,"lng":-122.41}, "destination": {...},
                        "rideType": "comfort", "paymentMethodId": "pm_..." }
-> 202 { "tripId": "t_9f3c", "state": "matching", "fareEstimate": {"amount": 1840,"currency":"USD","surge":1.4},
         "etaSeconds": 240, "candidatesPending": true }

GET  /v1/rides/{tripId}   -> { state, driver: {...}, driverLocation: {...}, etaPickupSeconds, fareEstimate }
POST /v1/rides/{tripId}/cancel   { "reason": "..." }
POST /v1/rides/{tripId}/rate     { "rating": 5, "tipCents": 300 }
```
Driver
```
POST /v1/driver/location   { "lat":..., "lng":..., "heading":..., "speed":..., "ts":... }  -> 204 (fire and forget)
PUT  /v1/driver/status      { "available": true, "vehicleType": "comfort" }
POST /v1/offers/{offerId}/accept  -> 204   (the offer, delivered out-of-band)
GET  /v1/offers?lat&lng      -> pending offers
```

Assignment contract (internal, and the important one)
```
POST /internal/assign
  { "tripId": "t_9f3c", "riderId": "u_1", "origin": {...}, "requiredVehicleType": "comfort",
    "maxPickupSeconds": 600, "excludedDriverIds": ["u_9"], "surgeAllowed": true,
    "idempotencyKey": "t_9f3c" }
-> { "state": "assigned", "driverId": "u_55", "offerExpiresAt": 1767000030,
     "etaPickupSeconds": 240, "attempt": 1 }
```
Offer flow, which is where the product reality lives: the dispatcher does not simply assign the nearest driver; it **offers** to a driver, who has ~10 seconds to accept. So the state machine is `matching → offered → accepted | expired → offered(next candidate)`, and the whole match loop is a sequence of attempts. Every attempt is idempotent and time-bounded, and there is a hard cap on attempts (e.g. 8) after which the trip escalates to a different strategy.

Error cases
| Case | Behavior |
|---|---|
| No drivers within radius | widen the radius in steps; after N widenings, offer a longer pickup ETA honestly rather than pretending |
| Driver declines/expires | next candidate from the ranked list, respecting `excludedDriverIds` |
| Double assignment | optimistic version check on the trip; exactly one `accepted` wins; the loser is released and offered to other riders' trips |
| Fare dispute | the ledger entry is immutable; disputes create compensating entries |
| Surge disallowed by region/cap | clamp to the regional max multiplier |
| Payment fails | trip completes, payment retries, driver is paid from a float — never hold the driver hostage |

---

**5. Data model**

```
geo_grid                       -- in-memory + Redis, ephemeral, the hot path
  cell = geohash precision 6 (~1.2 km x 0.6 km)
  cell:{g}  -> LIST of driver_id with a TTL and a last-known position bucket
  TTL 30s -- a driver that stops reporting disappears
```
```
driver_state
  driver_id, vehicle_type, available, last_location, last_seen, rating, earnings_today
  INDEX (last_location.geohash5, available)
```
```
trips
  trip_id, rider_id, driver_id, state, origin, destination, requested_at,
  matched_at, pickup_eta_s, pickup_at, dropoff_at, distance_m, duration_s,
  fare_base, surge_multiplier, surge_reason, fare_total, payment_state,
  version   -- optimistic concurrency for state transitions
```
```
trip_offers
  offer_id, trip_id, driver_id, offered_at, expires_at, attempt, response
  INDEX (driver_id, response, expires_at)
```
```
surge_history
  cell, minute, demand, supply, multiplier      -- the audit trail for the price users see
```
```
ledger                      -- append-only, double-entry, immutable
  entries(txn_id, account, amount_cents, currency, kind, trip_id, created_at)
```
```
service_areas               -- city polygons, geofences, surge caps per city
```

Key modeling choices to defend
- **Trip state as an explicit state machine with an optimistic version**, not free-form flags. Illegal transitions must be impossible or at least detectable.
- **Location data is ephemeral and TTL'd**; it is *not* in the trips table, and it is not the source of truth for anything financial.
- **Surge history is persisted**, because "why was my fare 2.1×" must be answerable months later with the exact demand/supply numbers at that minute.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Driver app --POST /driver/location--> Location Ingest (Kafka: topic per geocell)
                                          |
                                          v
                                   Geo Grid (in-memory, sharded by geocell)
                                          |
Rider app --POST /rides--> Trip Service --> Dispatcher <--- Geo Grid (candidate drivers)
                            |                |
                            |                +--> Ranking (ETA, rating, fairness, fatigue)
                            |                +--> Offer Service --push--> driver apps
                            |                +--> attempt loop with timeout/backoff
                            v
                       Trip State Store (sharded, strong per trip)
                            |
                            +--> Notification/updates --push--> rider app
                            +--> Kafka -> Billing/Ledger, Analytics, Surge Aggregator
                                         |
                                         v
                               Surge/Pricing Engine (per geocell, 1-min buckets)
                                         |
                                         v
                                ETA Service (cached, per OD-pair)
```

The dispatcher is a short, latency-sensitive loop over two inputs: candidate drivers from the geo grid, and an ETA service. Everything else (pricing, analytics, notifications) is off the critical path.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Greedy nearest vs batch matching**

- **Greedy (assign the nearest available driver to each request as it arrives)** is optimal for the *current* request and terrible for total efficiency: it strands drivers in clusters, produces longer waits for later riders, and increases deadhead mileage. It is simple and it is what most MVPs do.
- **Batch matching** considers a *window* of unassigned requests and available drivers together and solves an assignment problem (Hungarian algorithm / min-cost flow on a small region-partitioned graph). Typical gain: **5–15% reduction in rider wait and driver deadhead**, at the cost of a short delay (200–500 ms batching) and much higher compute.
- The real system is **hybrid**: continuous dispatch in most areas, with periodic micro-batching (every 2–5 s) in dense zones where the efficiency gain is real, and greedy in sparse areas where batching would just add latency.
- The deeper insight: the *objective* is not "minimize the rider's wait." It is to minimize the system-wide total: rider wait + driver idle time + deadhead + cancellation probability. A locally-greedy assignment that gives the nearest driver a 2-minute pickup can be globally worse than giving a slightly farther driver a 90-second pickup. So the dispatcher scores candidates on a **multi-objective function**: predicted pickup ETA, driver rating (customer-experience weight), driver earnings fairness (a fairness term that prevents the same drivers from winning every trip and then going offline), cancellation probability, and a fatigue limit (no 11th consecutive hour without a break).
- Also: **don't offer a driver whose return trip will be empty**. In NYC a midtown pickup heading downtown at 5 p.m. is worth much more than the 300 m closer driver heading uptown. So ETA-to-destination and return-demand enter the score.

**7.2 Geo indexing, the location stream, and battery**

- **Geohash grid at precision 6** (~1.2 km × 0.6 km cells) as the primary structure: cheap to update, cheap to query, and cheap to shard by cell. Alternatives and why they lose: H3 hexagons give better distance accuracy and uniformity at the cost of a client library and more expensive queries; quadtrees need rebalancing; geohash precision-7 cells are ~150 m and 10× the cell count for marginal accuracy.
- **In-memory grid with TTL**, per region, is what makes matching fast: a driver that stops reporting vanishes in 30 s without any cleanup job. The grid is fed by a Kafka topic partitioned by geocell, so updates to the same cell land on the same consumer and the grid is consistent per cell.
- **Adaptive location frequency** is the battery answer: 1 update per second while moving and carrying a passenger, 5–15 seconds while cruising, 30–60 seconds while idle, and 0 when the driver app is backgrounded (only a coarse "available" heartbeat). That alone cuts location volume by 60%+ with no product impact.
- Map matching: raw GPS is noisy and lies near tall buildings, so positions are map-matched onto the road network. Do that **once**, server-side, before indexing, and cache the matched position on the driver record. Doing it per-request is a CPU sink.
- Batch driver locations in the response: one trip-state read returns the driver's last known position, so the rider app polls one endpoint rather than two.

**7.3 Surge pricing: the mechanism, not just the multiplier**

Surge is a **feedback controller**, and saying that explicitly is the differentiator.
- **Demand**: requests per geocell per minute (including requests that were cancelled for lack of supply). **Supply**: available drivers in the cell. Ratio drives the multiplier.
- The controller: `multiplier = clamp(f(demand/supply), 1.0, regional_cap)` with **hysteresis and a time constant** — a multiplier that reacts instantly oscillates and shocks users; a 5–10 minute window with gradual steps (e.g. 0.25 increments) is standard. Ramp down slower than ramp up.
- **Anticipation**: because riders respond to price, a naive controller overshoots. Real systems forecast demand 15–30 minutes ahead (weather, events, subway outages, holidays) and raise price *before* the spike, which reduces the spike. That is a genuine ML problem and mentioning it distinguishes you.
- **Supply elasticity lag**: raising the price brings drivers in, but they need 5–15 minutes to arrive. So the controller must be slow relative to supply elasticity or it will spike and then collapse.
- **Product guardrails**, all of which are real requirements: a cap (typically 2–3×, lower in some jurisdictions), geographic and temporal hysteresis, an explanation in the UI ("high demand in your area"), a "wait and save" option showing the price if the user delays, and legal/safety review — surge has caused public and regulatory backlash, so the multiplier is auditable (the `surge_history` table is the audit trail).
- **Never surge on a straight supply/demand ratio alone**: a citywide event spike can push every cell to the cap simultaneously. Cap per cell, and suppress surges inside event geofences entirely.

**7.4 Failure modes at surge: the system breaks where you least expect**

- **Positioning collapse** is the failure everyone should name: the graph problem of finding a route with a driver nearby is *not* the same as finding a driver on a fast route, because one route attracts other drivers, which attracts riders, which attracts drivers — a feedback loop. Real systems use a modified cost that penalizes routes inside a congested zone, and use **"rolling trips"** (a driver heading toward a demand hotspot with no passenger gets a compensating payment) to move supply before it is needed.
- **Assignment storms**: a large event creates thousands of requests in one cell. The dispatcher must **shard by cell** and shed load gracefully (widen radius, offer longer ETAs, route to a "surge queue" with honest ETA) rather than queueing everything in one in-memory buffer that OOMs.
- **Offer cascades**: a driver declines and the next offer generates a push notification storm. Fix: a per-driver offer rate limit and a **negative cache on recently-declined drivers** (do not re-offer the same trip for 2 minutes), which cuts wasted notifications dramatically.
- **ETA service saturation**: ETA is the dispatcher's inner loop, so it must be precomputed per (origin cell, destination cell, vehicle, traffic bucket) and cached, never computed per candidate. Computing a road-network path for 500 candidates per request is the classic mistake that makes dispatch slow exactly when volume is highest.
- **Dispatch starvation of a region**: the dispatcher must be **cell-partitioned** so a surge in Times Square cannot delay dispatch in San Francisco.
- **Payment failure, driver income loss**: if a trip cannot be paid, the trip still completes and the driver is paid from a float; the debt is collected later. Never make a driver wait on payment.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**
