1. **Location ingest (2M writes/sec)** — the largest volume, the lowest value. Solutions in order: adaptive frequency, hierarchical aggregation, coarser storage in Redis, hot-driver batching.
2. **Surge spikes**: demand can 10× in minutes (concert ending, subway outage). Cell-sharded dispatchers plus horizontal autoscale on *request rate per cell*, not on average CPU.
3. **ETA accuracy under traffic change**: cache invalidation. Cached ETAs must be scoped to a traffic bucket with a 60–120 s TTL; serving a stale ETA from 3 a.m. at 5 p.m. destroys trust. Fall back to a free-flow ETA that is explicitly optimistic rather than a wrong one.
4. **Offer fan-out latency**: driver push delivery is the long pole (APNs/FCM can take 1–5 s). Use multiple channels in parallel — push, then socket, then in-app poll — and treat push as best-effort. Optimize the expiry window to cover realistic notification latency.
5. **Match fairness** causing driver dissatisfaction and churn. Fairness must be in the scoring function, and you must monitor earnings dispersion by neighborhood to detect a feedback loop.
6. **Cold-start cities**: no historical demand data. Bootstrap with fleet size targets and conservative multipliers, and ramp surge ceilings slowly.
7. **Double-assignment races** at the trip state store; solved with optimistic versioning and idempotency keys, but the retry logic must be airtight.
8. **Data privacy**: precise location traces are legally sensitive; retention limits (2 hours hot, aggregate beyond) and regional regulatory differences must be enforced in the architecture, not in a wiki.
9. **Cancellation cascades** — surge causes cancellations which cause supply loss which raises surge further. Break the loop with hysteresis and by suppressing surge inside geofenced event areas.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Geo grid node down | matching for those cells stops | shard by cell so the blast radius is geographic; ETA cache still serves |
| ETA service down | fall back to free-flow ETA, optimistic and labeled | cache; recompute on recovery |
| Dispatcher overloaded in a cell | shed load: widen radius, honest longer ETA, cap request queue | autoscale on per-cell QPS |
| Kafka (location) down | driver positions go stale; the grid keeps the last known point, which is wrong but usable | reconnect; the last-known position expires from the grid after 30 s |
| Push provider down | offers do not arrive; the trip waits, then re-offers to the next driver | multiple channels; treat offer expiry as a decline |
| Surge controller down | freeze at the last multiplier (do not recompute from partial data) | fail-safe: freeze, do not crash to 1.0 or to the cap |
| Trip store down | trips cannot transition | strong per-trip sharding; accept failed assignments over double assignments |
| Payment provider down | trips complete, fare goes to a receivable ledger entry | retry + collections; drivers paid from float |
| Map-matching service down | raw positions, more noisy | use raw; ETA accuracy degrades slightly |
| One region of a city fails | that region matches poorly or not at all | independent cell dispatch; regional isolation |
| Driver app crashes mid-trip | trip appears stuck | heartbeat timeout after 90 s reassigns, with a safety check for an in-progress trip |
| Fraud/bot riders spamming requests | surge spikes artificially | per-user rate limits, device fingerprint, ML fraud scoring before surge computation |
| Clock skew across driver devices | stale positions accepted | server-side receive time is authoritative, never device time |

The invariant: **a trip must never be assigned twice, and a trip must never be silently dropped.** Everything else may degrade.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Dispatch | hybrid: micro-batch in dense cells, greedy in sparse | pure batch | batch adds 200–500 ms latency for a 10% efficiency gain that only pays off where density is high |
| Objective | system-wide total (wait + idle + deadhead + fairness) | minimize rider wait only | locally optimal assignment is globally worse |
| Geo structure | geohash-6 grid, in-memory, TTL | H3 / quadtree / Postgres PostGIS | cheapest to update and shard; accuracy is sufficient at 600 m cells |
| ETA | precomputed per OD-cell pair, cached with traffic buckets | compute per candidate | pathfinding for 500 candidates per request is the classic bottleneck |
| Location frequency | adaptive 1 s–60 s by state | fixed 1 s | 60%+ volume reduction, negligible quality loss |
| Offer model | expiring offers with an attempt loop | direct assignment | drivers decline; a direct assignment produces silent cancellations |
| State store | sharded by trip with optimistic versioning | one global transaction | 463 trips/sec average does not need global serialization |
| Surge | feedback controller with hysteresis, anticipation, and a cap | threshold on the demand/supply ratio | prevents oscillation and user-hostile spikes |
| Surge data | persisted audit trail | computed on the fly | "why 2.1×?" must be answerable later |
| Fairness | in the scoring function | ignore it | driver churn if the same 5% win every trip |
| Financial data | append-only double-entry ledger | mutable balances | disputes, reversals, and audits demand immutability |
| Location retention | 2 hours hot, aggregated beyond | keep everything | legal/privacy exposure and cost |

Rejected wholesale
- **A relational DB with PostGIS doing "find the nearest driver" per request.** One query per request against a spatial index cannot survive 3k/sec in a single city, let alone globally.
- **Event-driven recomputation of surge from raw requests.** Noisy, lagging, and impossible to explain to a regulator.
- **Eliminating offers and auto-assigning.** Cancellation rate and driver trust both collapse.
- **Surge as a hard cap with no hysteresis.** Users experience it as arbitrary and hostile; it also drives cancellations that worsen the shortage.
- **Storing ETA in the request path.** It is the hottest computation in the system and must be precomputed.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "A driver is 200 m away but going the wrong way. Do you assign them?"**
Model answer: No, and this is where naive nearest-driver dispatch fails. Distance is not the objective; the objective is the system-wide total of rider wait plus driver idle time plus deadhead plus cancellation probability. A driver 200 m away pointing away from the rider at 5 mph has a predicted pickup ETA of 7 minutes and a high chance of canceling or accepting and never showing up, which is worse than a 600 m driver facing the rider at 30 mph with a 2-minute ETA. So the dispatcher scores candidates on predicted pickup ETA, direction alignment, rating, cancellation probability, a fatigue limit, and a fairness term. I would also include ETA to the rider's destination, because a driver whose trip ends in a dead zone will go offline right after, and routing a good pickup to a bad future is a real cost. The scoring is a small multi-objective function, tuned offline, and I weight it by density: in midtown the optimal assignment looks nothing like the optimal assignment in a suburb at 4 a.m. The failure mode to guard against is a scoring function tuned on average behavior that then systematically starves one neighborhood — I monitor earnings dispersion by neighborhood, because driver churn is the symptom.

**Q2. "How would you explain surge pricing to an angry user, and how do you set it?"**
Model answer: It is a feedback controller, and I would explain it that way. Every minute, per geocell, I compute demand — requests per minute including ones cancelled for no available driver — and supply, which is available drivers. The ratio drives a multiplier, clamped to a per-city cap, applied with hysteresis so it does not jump around. Ramping down is slower than ramping up, because supply responds to price with a 5–15 minute lag: if I raise sharply and then immediately lower, I get a visible spike-collapse oscillation that reads as arbitrary. Anticipation is the sophisticated part: I forecast 15–30 minutes ahead using weather, events, and transit outages, and raise price before the spike arrives, which reduces the spike instead of chasing it. For the user, I show the multiplier, the reason, the cap, and a "wait and save" option with the projected price — and critically I log every multiplier and its demand/supply inputs, so a dispute months later is answerable with data rather than recollection. And I suppress surge entirely inside event geofences, because surge during a crowd-out event is exactly when it stops being defensible.

**Q3. "How do you handle 10,000 requests arriving in one geocell in 3 minutes?"**
Model answer: First, no queue — an in-memory per-cell queue is how this OOMs. Requests are assigned to a cell-sharded dispatcher, so the surge is contained to that cell's workers and does not touch the rest of the city. Second, I widen the search radius in bounded steps rather than one huge query: 600 m, then 1 km, then 2 km, tracking how much wait time that buys, so I can tell the user an honest ETA instead of showing nothing. Third, offers fan out with a bounded parallel limit and a per-driver rate limit, plus a negative cache so a driver who declined does not get the same trip again for two minutes. Fourth, if supply is genuinely exhausted, the trip sits in a per-cell queue with a visible ETA and the surge multiplier, and the queue is bounded — when it fills, new requests are rejected with a clear message rather than accepted and never fulfilled. Fifth, the surge controller is deliberately slow and capped so the price does not whipsaw while the event plays out. And I break the feedback loop that normally makes it worse: a surge causes riders to cancel and drivers to go offline, which raises surge further. Hysteresis, a per-cell cap, a slow down-ramp, and event geofence suppression all exist to stop that spiral.

**Q4. "Why not just assign the nearest driver every time? What's actually better?"**
Model answer: Greedy nearest-driver is optimal for the current request and worse for the system, and the difference is not theoretical. Greedy strands drivers: it wins the closest driver for whoever asked first, so a cluster of requests drains one cell and leaves drivers idle next door, and later riders wait longer while a driver sits 300 m away. Total rider wait, deadhead miles, and cancellation rate are all worse, typically by 5–15%. Batching fixes it — solving a small assignment problem over a 2–5 second window with min-cost flow over a cell-partitioned bipartite graph — but it costs 200–500 ms of added latency and real compute, which is why the answer is hybrid: batch in dense zones where the efficiency gain exceeds the latency cost, greedy in sparse zones where it does not. And the deeper change is the objective. Minimizing the rider's wait is the wrong target; minimizing rider wait plus driver idle plus deadhead plus cancellation risk is right, and that objective also licenses a fairness term so the same drivers do not win every trip and then quit. That fairness term costs a little efficiency and is worth it, because driver churn removes supply and makes everyone's wait worse.

**Q5. "How do you handle a driver's phone dying mid-trip?"**
Model answer: There is a heartbeat on the trip, and the timeout is an explicit state-machine transition rather than a side effect. The driver app heartbeats every 10–15 seconds while in progress; if nothing arrives for 90 seconds, the system flags the trip as suspect and reassigns. Two safety checks happen first, because automatic reassignment to a trip where a passenger is physically in the car is a bad outcome: check whether the driver's position has moved materially (a stationary vehicle is more likely a dead phone than an abandoned rider), and check whether the pickup point is still near the driver's last known position — if the driver is 30 km away, this is not a dead phone, it is a handover situation. The rerouted driver gets the full context including the passenger's location and the original driver's last known position so they do not drive to the wrong place. Meanwhile, because the fare is already committed in the ledger, the passenger is not charged for the extra distance or time, and the driver who lost the trip is compensated from a float. So: heartbeat, two checks, transparent handoff, and the passenger's bill is corrected. The design principle is that a stuck trip is worse than a wasted driver trip.

**Q6. "How do you scale this to a new city with no data?"**
Model answer: Cold start means no demand history, no supply curve, and no fairness calibration, so the surge controller is the thing that will do damage if I turn it on unchanged. I bootstrap with a supply-side plan: seed drivers to reach a target median pickup ETA, and use a conservative flat multiplier of 1.0 until there is genuine evidence of shortage. Then I ramp surge ceilings slowly — start at a 1.2× cap, raise it only after weeks of clean data, because the first time a city sees a legitimate 3× price the local reaction is severe and hard to undo. Demand forecasting needs a prior rather than a model, so I lean on exogenous signals (events, weather, transit) and a day-of-week/time-of-day profile built from the first weeks, and I keep the controller in a conservative mode with wide hysteresis until the forecast error rate is acceptable. Operationally I run a shadow mode: compute what the multiplier *would* have been and log it without applying it, then review the distribution with the local operations team before enabling enforcement. And I would ship the fairness and fatigue constraints at launch rather than later, because those are policy decisions that get politically expensive to add retroactively once drivers have expectations.

---

## 12.5.12 Ticket booking (BookMyShow)

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design a ticket booking system for a movie theater chain. Users browse shows, pick seats, pay, and get a ticket. Seats must never be double-booked."

The interviewer is scoring: this is a **classic overselling/consistency** problem. The right answer is seat inventory as *short-lived reserved, expiring state* plus a *strong single-writer-per-show* model, plus payments as a **saga** with compensating actions, plus a high-volume spike problem. Most candidates build a CRUD app and miss every one of those.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Browse movies → showtimes → seat map.
- F2. Hold seats (temporary lock) with a countdown.
- F3. Pay, get a ticket with QR/barcode; cancel with refund rules.
- F4. Seat categories (regular, premium, recliner, couple) with different pricing.
- F5. Multi-seat booking and multi-person splits.
- F6. Showtime cancellation / refunds en masse.
- F7. Access control: age ratings, blocked screens, gendered seating (some markets).
- F8. Notifications (confirmation, cancellation).

Non-functional
- N1. **No double booking, ever.** This is the absolute constraint.
- N2. Seat-map load **< 200 ms** at peak (a popular show sells out in seconds).
- N3. Booking completion (hold → pay → confirm) **< 5 s**.
- N4. Seat holds expire (typically 5–10 minutes) and the inventory returns automatically.
- N5. Availability 99.95% at peak; a checkout outage costs real money.
- N6. Consistency scope: strong *within a showtime*, eventually consistent across shows.
- N7. Handle a launch spike: 50k users hitting the same showtime.

---

**3. Estimation**

Scale
- 800 screens, 8 shows/day → **6,400 showtimes/day**; peak is 8,000 concurrent screens' worth of booking activity in the evening window.
- Seats per show ~120 → total seat-inventory 6,400 × 120 = **768k seats/day**, ~9.9M seat-shows/month.
- Peak booking: 60% of tickets sold in the 4 hours before a weekend evening show. Say 100k concurrent booking sessions.

QPS
- Seat map loads dominate reads: 800k sessions × 4 seat-map views = **3.2M seat-map reads/day** ≈ 37 QPS average, **~2k QPS at peak** — because a session refreshes the map every few seconds. Cache aggressively.
- Booking attempts: 100k sessions peak × 1 hold attempt/minute = **1.7k/s**; successful bookings peak ~**300/s**.
- Holds: at peak, 2k concurrent holds × 10-minute TTL = 2k × 6 = **12k holds/minute** created and expired. That churn is the write load.

Storage
- Booking row ~1 KB; 100M bookings/year → **100 GB/year**. Trivial.
- Seat inventory: 768k seats/day × 90 days history = **69M seat-show rows**, ~200 B each = **14 GB**. Trivial volume, high contention.
- Payments ledger: append-only, immutable — the only thing that must never be lost.

Bandwidth
- Seat map JSON: 120 seats × ~60 B = **7 KB** per request. At 2k QPS = **14 MB/s**. Fine, but only if cached; a 7 KB response × uncached 2k QPS across 800 theaters is a lot of avoidable traffic.
- Confirmation emails: 100M/year, trivial volume, high deliverability sensitivity.

Arithmetic to state
- "Overselling by one seat on one show costs a refund plus a bad review. 100M bookings/year × 0.001% error = 1,000 double bookings. So the constraint isn't throughput, it's the correctness of the hold mechanism."

---

**4. API design**

```
GET /v1/movies?city=&date=                       -> movies with showtime counts
GET /v1/shows/{showId}/seats
-> { "showId": "s_88f3", "screen": "Screen 4", "startsAt": "2026-10-03T19:30:00Z",
     "categories": { "recliner": 550, "premium": 350, "regular": 250 },
     "layout": { "rows": ["A".."J"], "seatsPerRow": [12, ...] },
     "status": [ {"id":"A-1","state":"available","price":350,"category":"recliner"},
                 {"id":"A-2","state":"held","heldByYou":true,"expiresInSeconds":287},
                 {"id":"A-3","state":"sold","bookingId":"b_9912"} ],
     "version": 48213,          // optimistic concurrency token for the map
     "updatedAt": 1767000000 }
```

```
POST /v1/shows/{showId}/holds
{ "seatIds": ["A-1","A-2"], "idempotencyKey": "uuid" }
-> 201 { "holdId": "h_1a2b", "expiresAt": 1767000600, "amount": 700,
         "seats": ["A-1","A-2"], "quote": {...} }
409 { "error": "seats_unavailable", "conflicts": ["A-2"] }   // partial failure: be explicit which
422 { "error": "hold_limit_exceeded" }   // max 6 seats per booking
```

```
POST /v1/bookings
{ "holdId": "h_1a2b", "paymentMethodId": "pm_1", "contactEmail": "...",
  "idempotencyKey": "uuid" }
-> 201 { "bookingId": "b_9912", "status": "confirmed",
         "tickets": [ {"seat":"A-1","qr":"...","category":"recliner"} ],
         "amount": 700, "payment": {"state":"captured","txnId":"txn_44"} }
```

```
DELETE /v1/holds/{holdId}                 -> 204 (release early)
DELETE /v1/bookings/{bookingId}           -> 202 cancellation + refund policy applied
GET  /v1/bookings/{id}/ticket            -> QR payload (short-lived, signed)
```

Error semantics that matter
| Case | Response | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Seat already sold | 409 with `conflicts[]` | never silently drop a seat from a multi-seat booking |
| Hold expired during payment | 409 `hold_expired` | **the common real-world failure**; UI must handle it gracefully |
| Payment failed | 402, hold released immediately | do not keep the hold; another user is waiting |
| Payment succeeded, booking write failed | compensate: refund + release; never leave a captured payment with no ticket | saga, see §7.2 |
| Idempotent replay | return the original booking | mandatory — mobile clients retry |
| Stale `version` on seat map | 200 with `version` mismatch flag, or 409 on hold | optimistic concurrency |

---

**5. Data model**

The key insight: **seat state is not stored as a mutable "is_sold" boolean on a seat row; it is derived from bookings and holds, with holds in a TTL'd store.**

```
show_seat_state                   -- sharded by show_id (single writer per show)
  show_id      BIGINT
  seat_id      TEXT       -- "A-1"
  state        ENUM(available, held, sold, blocked)
  hold_id      TEXT NULL
  booking_id   TEXT NULL
  expires_at   TIMESTAMPTZ NULL
  price_cents  INT
  version      BIGINT     -- incremented on every state change
  PRIMARY KEY (show_id, seat_id)
```

```
holds
  hold_id, show_id, user_id, seat_ids[], amount_cents, expires_at, state
  INDEX (show_id, seat_id) WHERE state='active'
  TTL expires_at + 60s      -- hard expiry in the store
```

```
bookings
  booking_id, show_id, user_id, hold_id, seats[], amount_cents,
  state ENUM(pending_payment, confirmed, cancelled, refunded, failed),
  payment_txn_id, created_at, cancelled_at
  UNIQUE (show_id, seat_id) WHERE state = 'confirmed'      -- the real safety net
```

```
payments (append-only ledger, immutable)
  txn_id, booking_id, type ENUM(authorize, capture, refund, void),
  amount_cents, provider, provider_txn_id, created_at
```

```
Idempotency
  idempotency_key, scope, response_snapshot, created_at, TTL 24h
```

Why the `UNIQUE (show_id, seat_id) WHERE state='confirmed'` partial unique index is the crown jewel: it makes overselling **impossible at the database level**, so even if your application logic has a race, the database refuses the second booking. Every serious answer should end with this. Also note: hold state can be **fully in Redis** (it is ephemeral), while *confirmed* bookings live in Postgres with a unique constraint.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
App: browse -> seat map -> hold -> pay -> ticket

Seat Map Service (per-show shard)
   +--> Redis: cached seat map (TTL 2-5 s, invalidated on state change)
   +--> Postgres: show_seat_state (single-writer shard per show_id)
   +--> Seat state is read from Redis, written to Redis + Postgres asynchronously,
       with Postgres as the authority for confirmed bookings only

Booking Service
   1. validate hold (Redis, TTL) -> acquire an idempotency key
   2. create booking row state=pending_payment  (unique index guards seats)
   3. call Payment Gateway: authorize -> capture
   4. on success: booking -> confirmed, seats -> sold, emit Kafka (ticket, email, wallet)
   5. on failure: booking -> failed, seats -> available, hold released

Expiry Worker
   scans expired holds (time-bucketed, per show shard) -> release seats, emit events

Payment Gateway (with webhook + reconciliation)
Payments/Ledger (append-only) | Ticket/QR service (signed, short-lived) | Notification (Kafka -> email/SMS)
```

Diagram: per-**show** sharding is the key structural choice. It concentrates contention into one shard, gives single-writer semantics for free, and bounds blast radius to one show. It also means *uneven load* across shows, which is handled by moving hot shows to their own shard.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Two-phase reservation: the hold is the whole trick**

The entire correctness story is: **reserve with a TTL, then confirm durably.**
1. `POST /holds` — atomically flip the selected seats to `held` with `expires_at = now + 600 s`. If any seat is not `available`, fail and tell the user exactly which seats conflicted.
2. The seat map is cached in Redis for a few seconds, so other users see the hold immediately. **Broadcast a targeted invalidation** to the other viewers of that show so the UI updates within ~1 s (websocket or just a short cache TTL).
3. Expiry is the store's job: a TTL'd Redis key plus a sweeper. **Never rely on the client to release**, because clients disappear.
4. `POST /bookings` — the hold must still be alive at payment time. This is the most common real-world failure: the user's payment gateway takes 90 seconds and the hold expired. Handle it explicitly: return `409 hold_expired`, and design the payment flow so the *authorization* is fast and the capture happens after the booking commits.
5. On confirm, flip `held → sold` and record the booking. The partial unique index `(show_id, seat_id) WHERE state='confirmed'` guarantees no double sale even under a bug.

Capacity and fairness details worth mentioning: cap the number of seats per booking (typically 6) so one user cannot hoard a sold-out show; cap **holds per user per show** (1–2) to stop hold-stuffing by bots; and when inventory falls below a threshold (say 10% remaining), switch from "best available" suggestions to "no auto-select" to reduce bot efficiency. Bot/abuse prevention is a real requirement — ticket scalping bots are a genuine operational problem — and hold limits plus per-user payment instrument limits are the practical controls.

**7.2 Payment as a saga, not a transaction**

You cannot use a database transaction across a payment provider. Model it as a saga with explicit states:
```
hold seats -> authorize payment -> confirm booking -> capture payment -> issue tickets
```
Every step has a compensating action:
| Step fails | Compensation |
|---|---|
| authorize fails | release seats, mark booking failed |
| booking write fails after authorize | **void** the authorization; do not capture |
| capture fails after booking confirmed | retry capture; if permanently failed, cancel booking + release seats + notify |
| ticket/email fails | no compensation needed — tickets are recoverable from the booking |
| everything succeeds | nothing |
- **Ordering rule**: never capture before the booking is durable. Capture-then-confirm means you have money with no ticket, which is a refund and a support ticket. Authorize-then-confirm-then-capture means the worst case is a void.
- **Idempotency keys everywhere.** Both to the payment provider and to your own booking endpoint. A retried request must never produce two bookings or two captures.
- **Webhook reconciliation**: providers are unreliable about webhooks. A reconciliation job compares the immutable ledger against the provider's daily settlement file and produces alerts for anything mismatched. This is the one place where you need to genuinely reconcile daily, not just rely on events.
- **Money correctness**: the ledger is append-only double-entry, balances are derived, and a refund is a new compensating entry, never a mutation. Give it a separate, slower-replicated store than booking state, because bookings need strong consistency and the ledger needs durability and auditability — those are different priorities and mixing them is a mistake.

**7.3 Seat-map reads at peak: this is 95% of your traffic**

- Seat map is the same bytes for every viewer of a show except for the seats *they* hold. So: cache one shared seat map in Redis keyed by `show_id` (TTL 2–5 s), and merge the requester's own holds on top after reading the cache. That is one cache read plus a small local merge, and it works regardless of how many people are watching.
- **Invalidation on state change**: publish to a Redis pub/sub or Kafka topic per show; subscribers in the gateway layer delete the local cache. Plus the TTL as the backstop. Do not rely on TTL alone — a 5-second-stale seat map is the difference between a user picking seat B-7 and getting a conflict at payment.
- **Websocket push for the show's viewers** (an "X just took B-7" event) so the seat map feels live; this is also what stops two people clicking the same seat simultaneously.
- The payload is small (120 seats ≈ 7 KB) so a single Redis GET is enough; do not build a per-show cache with per-seat granularity, which explodes the key count for no benefit.
- **Consistency honesty**: seat-map reads are allowed to be up to ~2 seconds stale; **seat claims are strongly consistent**. Say this explicitly, because it is the exact boundary you are designing and interviewers like hearing it named.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **The hot show.** One popular showtime gets thousands of concurrent booking sessions; it is a single shard and a single row-group hotspot. Fixes: dedicate a shard to hot shows, cap hold attempts per user, and add a **virtual queue** for the checkout path on very hot shows.
2. **Hold expiry churn** — 12k holds/minute at peak, each a write plus a TTL event. Fix: holds live entirely in Redis; only confirmed bookings reach Postgres.
3. **Seat-map cache invalidation storms** — 2k QPS with frequent invalidations means a low hit rate if TTLs are short. Fix: pub/sub invalidation (fast, targeted) with a longer TTL (10–30 s) as backstop, and coalesce invalidations for a show into one message per second.
4. **Payment gateway latency and rate limits** — the checkout critical path depends on a third party. Fix: async where possible (authorize fast, capture async), connection pooling, per-provider circuit breakers, and always show a "processing" state rather than blocking on the provider's response.
5. **Bot hold-stuffing** on high-demand shows. Fix: per-user hold caps, per-instrument limits, device/IP rate limits, and a step-up (OTP) before payment on high-risk bookings.
6. **The expiry sweeper's own contention** — must be time-bucketed and sharded by show, not a full scan; a `WHERE expires_at < now()` without an index is a production incident.
7. **Notification volume** at showtime start (100M/year). Fix: asynchronous via Kafka, and never let email failure roll back a confirmed booking.
8. **Refund storms** on a cancelled show. Fix: batch refunds asynchronously, and be able to compute the exact refund policy per booking without a join to the original payment.
9. **Multi-region**: shows are geographically local, so book in-region; a global event (a stadium booking) is the exception and needs a single-writer region for that show.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Booking service down | **holds and bookings stop; seat maps keep serving** | reads and caching are independent, so browsing is unaffected |
| Payment provider down | checkout fails cleanly, holds released immediately | circuit breaker, alternate provider, retry at capture |
| Capture fails after confirmation | booking confirmed, payment pending | retry queue + reconciliation + customer notification; do not auto-cancel a ticketed customer |
| Redis down | seat maps fall back to Postgres (slower); **holds are lost** → must refuse new holds rather than risk double-booking | fail closed on holds, fail open on reads |
| Seat-map cache stale | user gets a 409 at hold | acceptable and expected; the UI shows "seat just taken" |
| Expiry sweeper down | seats appear held longer than 10 minutes | the TTL in the store still releases them; the sweeper is only for audit and promptness |
| Postgres primary failover | no new bookings for 30–60 s | replica promotion; holds keep expiring safely |
| Kafka down | tickets, emails, wallet credits delayed | no data loss; reconciliation |
| Ticket/QR service down | booking confirmed, QR unavailable | ticket retrievable from the booking; regenerate on demand |
| Bot attack on a hot show | holds exhausted by bots | hold caps, queue, step-up auth |
| Overbooking attempted by a bug | **database refuses** via the partial unique index | the safety net holds; alert loudly |
| Partial multi-seat hold | explicit conflict list | never silently drop seats |
| Clock skew on hold expiry | early/late expiry by the skew | Redis server time is authoritative, never the app server's |

Invariant: **never capture money without a durable booking; never release a hold based on client input; never allow a second confirmed booking for a seat** — the last one is enforced by the database, not by code.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Reservation | TTL holds in Redis + durable confirmed bookings | direct sell (no hold) | users abandon checkout constantly; without holds, inventory is destroyed by abandoned carts |
| Correctness | partial unique index as the final gate | application-level locking only | code has bugs; the DB does not |
| Seat state | derived from holds + bookings, held in Redis | mutable `is_sold` boolean | holds need TTL and atomic multi-seat claims; a boolean row cannot do that cleanly |
| Sharding | by `show_id` (single writer per show) | by seat_id or globally | concentrates contention, gives free single-writer semantics, bounds blast radius |
| Seat-map reads | shared cache + per-user overlay | per-user seat maps | one cache read scales to millions of viewers |
| Cache invalidation | pub/sub targeted + TTL backstop | TTL only | a 5-second-stale map is a bad experience |
| Payment ordering | authorize → confirm → capture | capture first | worst case becomes a void, not a refund |
| Money | append-only double-entry ledger | mutable balance rows | disputes and audits require immutability |
| Excess demand | queue + caps + no oversell | oversell and hope | oversell is a refund, a bad review, and a regulatory issue in some markets |
| Reads consistency | eventually consistent (~2 s) | strong | reads do not need strong consistency; claims do |
| Refunds | async batched with policy snapshot | synchronous | refunds must not block user flows |
| Seats per booking | capped (6) | uncapped | prevents hoarding and scalping |

Rejected wholesale
- **A single global inventory service with a mutex per show.** Correct, but a single point of failure with no sharding story.
- **Optimistic UI-only seat selection with no hold.** Guarantees conflict at checkout and destroys conversion.
- **A blockchain/NFT "ticket" model.** Solves a problem nobody has and adds enormous latency and cost.
- **Storing seat maps in a relational table with a row per seat and updating them per hold.** 768k seat-rows/day with heavy update contention; the Redis overlay plus partial index is strictly better.
- **Making seat-map reads go to the primary database.** 2k QPS × a 7 KB scan is an avoidable outage.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "How do you guarantee two users can never book the same seat?"**
Model answer: Four layers, and the last one is the one I actually trust. One, a hold with a TTL claims the seats atomically — Redis `SETNX`-style multi-key claim or a Lua script — so the second user is told immediately rather than at payment. Two, seat maps are served from a shared cache invalidated on state change, so users see each other's holds within a second or two. Three, the booking write is guarded by a partial unique index on `(show_id, seat_id) WHERE state='confirmed'` in Postgres, so even a race in my application code or a replayed request cannot produce two confirmed bookings for one seat — the database simply rejects the second insert. Four, idempotency keys on both the hold and the booking endpoints, so a mobile client retry is a no-op returning the original result rather than a second booking. The honest summary is that layers one through three are about user experience and error rates, and layer three is about correctness. If I had to keep only one mechanism it would be the unique index, because application-level concurrency control is where double-booking bugs actually come from.

**Q2. "What if the payment succeeds but the booking write fails?"**
Model answer: That ordering should never happen, because I sequence the payment as authorize → commit booking → capture. Authorization reserves funds without moving them; the booking commit is a local durable write; only then do I capture. So the failure mode I need to handle is: authorize succeeds, booking write fails. The compensation is a **void** of the authorization, which releases the hold, and I mark the booking row `failed` with the authorization id for audit. The reverse ordering — capture first — is the one that produces money-without-ticket, which is a refund and a support ticket, so I consider it a design error rather than a failure case. For the residual cases I still need to handle: capture fails after the booking is confirmed, which is a retry-queue situation and I deliberately do *not* auto-cancel a ticketed customer; the ticket or email fails, which needs no compensation because the ticket is always retrievable from the booking; and the webhook never arrives, which is why I have a daily reconciliation job that compares the immutable ledger against the provider's settlement file. Money lives in an append-only double-entry ledger with derived balances, so refunds are compensating entries, never mutations.

**Q3. "How do you handle a sold-out show? 50,000 people want 12 seats."**
Model answer: The seat map is the truth, so the failure mode is not overselling, it is 50,000 people hammering the hold endpoint for 12 seats. I cap holds per user per show at one or two, cap seats per booking at six, and require step-up authentication before payment for high-risk patterns — that alone kills most scalping. In the booking service, I shard by show, so the whole load lands on one shard that I can autoscale and rate-limit specifically. For the extreme case I put a virtual queue in front of checkout for shows below a remaining-inventory threshold: the user gets a position and an estimated wait, which converts a wall of 409s into a tolerable experience and dramatically reduces pointless retry load. Below 10% inventory I disable auto-select suggestions, because that is what makes bots efficient. And critically, I do not oversell. The alternative — selling 20 seats for 12 and refunding 8 — destroys trust, is a compliance problem in some markets, and generates exactly the cancellation and complaint volume that makes operations expensive. Queue, cap, and be honest.

**Q4. "How does this scale when a blockbuster drops at midnight?"**
Model answer: Read and write paths degrade differently, so they need different answers. Reads — the seat map — are served from a shared Redis cache keyed by show, with per-viewer overlays merged on top, so 50,000 viewers cost 50,000 cheap cache reads rather than 50,000 database queries. That path is essentially free. Writes are the problem: everyone tries to hold the same 200 seats in the first ninety seconds. I shard by show, so the load concentrates on one shard I can scale and rate-limit, and I add the virtual queue for shows under low inventory. The hold endpoint is where bots live, so it gets the hardest rate limiting: per-user hold caps, per-instrument caps, device and IP limits, and a queue. One thing worth doing early is warming — pre-creating the show and seat rows in advance of the drop, so midnight is not when the show shard is first asked to materialize 200 rows. And I would watch the ratio of holds to successful bookings; if it climbs, that is the bot signal, and I can tighten hold TTLs or add a step-up challenge rather than let the inventory be picked clean by an automated system.

**Q5. "What does an expiry sweeper look like, and why not just let Redis TTLs do it?"**
Model answer: Redis TTLs do the correctness work — a hold key with an `EXPIRE` disappears on its own, and if I model seat state so that a seat is held only while its hold key exists, expiry is automatic. The sweeper exists for the things a TTL cannot do. It has to write `available` back to the Postgres seat state so the durable record is correct, because Redis is the fast path and Postgres is the audit trail. It has to emit an event so that other viewers' cached seat maps get invalidated; otherwise a seat stays visually greyed out for up to the cache TTL after it is genuinely free, which is a conversion loss. And it has to record expiry for analytics — hold-to-purchase conversion is a key business metric. It must also be time-bucketed and sharded by show, because a `WHERE expires_at < now()` without an index is exactly the kind of full scan that takes down a service during a peak. So the design is: TTL for correctness, sweeper for consistency and observability. If the sweeper dies, seats still return to the pool — the worst case is a durable record that is stale for a while, and the cache TTL papers over the user-visible part.

**Q6. "How do you cancel a show and refund 4,000 people?"**
Model answer: As an explicit, idempotent batch job rather than a loop in a request handler. First, flip the show to `cancelled`, which makes every seat-map read return `cancelled` and every booking endpoint reject new sales immediately — that single flag stops new money from coming in. Then the job pages through the bookings for that show in keyset batches of a few hundred, computes each refund from a policy snapshot stored on the booking at purchase time — I never recompute policy at refund time, because the policy may have changed and the customer is entitled to what was quoted. Each refund is a compensating ledger entry plus a provider call with an idempotency key, so re-running the job cannot double-refund. Runs are recorded per booking, so a retry resumes rather than restarts. Notifications go through Kafka to a dedicated queue with per-recipient rate limiting, because 4,000 simultaneous cancellation emails will otherwise damage the sending reputation and land the real communications in spam. And there is a second wave after the batch: an out-of-band alert to anyone with an open session, since a user whose show was cancelled mid-checkout needs to know immediately, not at the batch's pace. Finally, refund turnaround time is a customer-facing SLA, so it gets its own dashboard and paging.

---

## 12.5.13 E-commerce (Amazon)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design an e-commerce catalog and checkout for Amazon. Hundreds of millions of products, flash sales, and it must never oversell or lose an order."

The score is in: **product catalog is a document problem, not a relational one**; **inventory is a hot-row problem**; the **order saga** across payment, inventory, shipping, and fraud; and **flash-sale load shedding**.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Product catalog: browse, search, facets, variations (size/color), related items.
- F2. Cart, multi-vendor (marketplace) support.
- F3. Checkout with address, payment, shipping options, tax.
- F4. Order placement, order history, returns, refunds, cancellations.
- F5. Seller onboarding and listing management.
- F6. Reviews, Q&A, availability by seller/region.
- F7. Recommendations, "frequently bought together".

Non-functional
- N1. **Never oversell**, never lose a placed order, never double-charge.
- N2. Catalog read: **< 100 ms p99**, search **< 300 ms p99**.
- N3. Checkout completion **< 5 s** excluding 3rd-party payment round trips.
- N4. Flash sales: 100k concurrent buyers on one SKU.
- N5. Availability 99.99% for checkout; 99.9% for browse.
- N6. Eventual consistency is fine for browse/search; strong consistency for inventory reservation and payment.
- N7. Seller-facing data isolation (a seller sees only their own data).

---

**3. Estimation**

Catalog
- 500M+ products across sellers, 30M+ sellers. Product documents average 4 KB (attributes, images refs, description, variations) → **2 TB** of catalog data. Small; the problem is *shape* and *indexing*, not volume.
- Write rate: 10M listing updates/day = **116/sec**. Reads: browse/search vastly higher.
- Search: 200k QPS peak; Solr/Elasticsearch at ~5k QPS per shard → 40–80 shards. Index size ~1–2 TB.

Traffic
- 300M orders/year (Amazon-like) = **3,470 orders/sec average**, ~10k/sec on Prime Day.
- Flash sale: 100k concurrent buyers on one SKU, 10k checkouts in the first 60 s → **~170 checkouts/sec on one SKU** — a single hot partition on purpose.
- Peak overall traffic ~10× average.

Storage
- Order record ~3 KB → 3,470/sec × 3 KB = 10 MB/s = **900 GB/day**, 330 TB/year. Order history is a large, append-heavy, queryable store → relational per-entity with an analytical replica.
- Inventory: 500M products × warehouse rows. Inventory **hot-row problem**: one row per SKU per warehouse updated thousands of times per second.

Bandwidth
- 500k QPS browse × 30 KB = **15 GB/s**. Again the CDN/caching layer is the answer.
- Images: entirely CDN.

Arithmetic to state
- "170 checkouts/sec on one SKU is the only interesting number in this design. Everything else is horizontal scaling; that one partition requires reservation semantics, not more machines."

---

**4. API design**

```
GET  /catalog/products/{asin}?marketplaceId=     -> full product document
GET  /catalog/search?q=&filters=&page=          -> search service (Solr/ES)
GET  /catalog/products/{asin}/offers?           -> price/offer list, refreshed < 5 min
GET  /cart?userId=                              -> cart service
PUT  /cart/{userId}/items/{asin}                { "qty": 2, "sellerId": "s_9" }
POST /checkout/sessions
     { "items": [{"asin":"B0...", "qty":1, "sellerId":"s_9"}],
       "addressId":"addr_1", "paymentMethodId":"pm_1", "shippingOptionId":"ship_2",
       "idempotencyKey":"uuid" }
-> 201 { "checkoutSessionId":"cs_1", "status":"requires_confirmation",
         "quote": {"itemsTotal":4999,"shipping":0,"tax":420,"total":5419,"quoteExpiresAt":...},
         "inventoryReserved": true, "fraudCheck":"pass" }
POST /checkout/sessions/{id}/confirm            { "idempotencyKey":"uuid" }
-> 201 { "orderId":"o_77", "status":"confirmed", "paymentState":"captured",
         "inventoryState":"committed", "shipments":[{"shipmentId":"sh_1"}] }
```

```
GET  /orders/{orderId}
POST /orders/{orderId}/cancel
POST /orders/{orderId}/return  { "items":[...], "reason":"..." }
POST /webhooks/payments         (provider callbacks, signature-verified)
```

Error semantics
| Case | Code | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Out of stock | 409 `out_of_stock` | with waitlist option |
| Item price changed | 409 `price_changed`, return new quote | never silently charge a different amount |
| Quote expired | 409 `quote_expired` | re-quote |
| Fraud blocked | 202 `pending_review` | order holds, do not fail the user outright |
| Inventory reserved but payment fails | 202 `payment_pending`, reservation auto-expires | saga compensation |
| Payment captured but order write fails | compensate: refund | **never keep money with no order** |
| Seller suspended mid-checkout | 409 with the offending item | checkout filters suspended sellers |
| Idempotent replay | return the original order | mandatory |
| High load / flash sale | 503 with `Retry-After` + queue position | better than 500s |

---

**5. Data model**

```
products                     -- document store, sharded by asin
  asin, title, brand, category_path, attributes{}, description,
  variations[] (size/color matrix), media_refs[], seller_ids[],
  rating_avg, review_count, search_vector, updated_at
  INDEX (category_path, brand) -- or a separate facet store
  denormalized, no FK to seller/inventory
```
Why a document store: products have wildly varying attributes, are read as a whole far more often than queried by attribute, and have a slow write path with a hot read path. A relational design would need EAV tables or a schema per category.

```
offers / sellers
  asin, seller_id, price_cents, shipping_cents, condition, fulfill_by,
  availability (enum), updated_at
```
```
inventory                   -- the hot path
  asin, warehouse_id, available, reserved, safety_stock, version
  PRIMARY KEY (asin, warehouse_id)
```
```
orders
  order_id, user_id, created_at, state, total_cents, currency,
  address_snapshot JSONB,        -- snapshot, never a live FK to an address book
  payment_txn_ids[], inventory_txn_ids[],
  channel (web/mobile/marketplace), seller_ids[]
  state ENUM(draft, placed, payment_pending, confirmed, picking, shipped,
             delivered, cancelled, refunding, refunded)
```
```
order_events                -- append-only state transition log (the saga journal)
  order_id, seq, from_state, to_state, reason, actor, created_at
```
```
idempotency
  idempotency_key, scope, response_snapshot, created_at
```
```
fraud_signals / risk_scores
  user_id, order_id, score, signals[], decided_at
```

Key choices to defend: **address and price are snapshots on the order**, not references; `order_events` is an append-only journal so every state transition is reconstructable; and **inventory has `version` for optimistic concurrency** rather than row locks held across a payment call.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Client
  +--> Catalog Service (document store + facet store + cache)         [read-heavy]
  +--> Search Service (Solr/ES cluster, 40-80 shards)                 [read-heavy]
  +--> Cart Service (Redis-backed, sticky to user)
  +--> Checkout Orchestrator (the saga)  <-- the interesting part
        1. quote: fetch offers + prices, compute tax/shipping
        2. inventory RESERVE (atomic, TTL 10 min)   -- Inventory Service
        3. fraud check (sync, < 300 ms, model + rules)
        4. payment AUTHORIZE (provider)              -- Payment Service
        5. persist order (Postgres, strong)
        6. inventory COMMIT, payment CAPTURE, fulfillment create
        7. emit Kafka: order.confirmed -> email, seller notification, tracking,
           invoices, analytics, recommendations
  +--> Inventory Service (hot-row optimized, partition by asin)
  +--> Payment Service (provider adapters, idempotent, ledger-backed)
  +--> Fulfillment/WMS (pick-pack-ship, warehouse system integration)
  +--> Order Service (Postgres per-entity + read replicas + analytical replica)
```

Checkout is a **saga with compensation**, and the diagram should show the states and the compensating arrows.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Inventory reservation and the hot row**

The classic failure: check `available > 0`, decrement, create order — and race two buyers into overselling.
- **Reserve, don't decrement.** `POST /inventory/reserve {asin, qty, orderId, ttl}` atomically moves `available → reserved` and returns a reservation id with a 10-minute TTL. Only after payment succeeds does `reserved → committed` (i.e. the sale). If payment fails or the TTL expires, a sweeper returns `reserved → available`.
- **Hot-row mitigation** for a flash sale on one SKU: (a) shard inventory by asin so the row is isolated, (b) **bucket the counter** — keep 100 units in a Redis token bucket per SKU, refill from the DB in 100s, so the DB write rate is 1% of the sell rate, (c) per-user purchase limits (e.g. 5 per SKU) which is also the anti-scalping control, (d) a **virtual queue** in front of checkout for high-demand SKUs so 100k buyers are queued rather than hammering one row, and (e) **read-committed + optimistic version** on the DB path as the correctness backstop.
- **Oversell is still possible in aggregate across warehouses.** Decide deliberately: allow oversell on low-demand SKUs (better conversion) and forbid it on high-demand ones (use the reservation queue). State this as a product decision with a technical mechanism.
- Also: never hold a database row lock across a payment-provider call. Reserve → release the lock → pay → commit. Locks held over network I/O are how a 200 ms payment call becomes a 30-second checkout stall.

**7.2 The checkout saga: states, compensations, idempotency**

States: `quoted → reserved → fraud_checked → authorized → order_persisted → confirmed`.
| Transition | Compensation on failure |
|---|---|
| reserve fails | nothing to undo; return out-of-stock |
| fraud fails | release reservation, mark `pending_review` if borderline |
| authorize fails | release reservation; do not persist an order |
| order persist fails | **void** the authorization; never capture before the order is durable |
| capture fails | retry; on hard failure, cancel the order and release inventory; notify |
| post-confirm steps fail | no compensation needed; the order is durable and downstream is recoverable via Kafka |

Idempotency discipline: every step gets a key derived from `orderId + step`; every compensating step is itself idempotent; Kafka consumers are idempotent by storing processed `eventId`s; and the checkout endpoint returns the original response for a replayed `idempotencyKey` — mandatory, because mobile clients retry and a duplicated order is a support incident and a double charge.

**7.3 Catalog: why a document store, and how search stays fast**

- **Products as documents**: heterogeneous attributes per category, read-whole on the product page, slow writes, hot reads. A document store (or a Postgres `JSONB` if you want relational simplicity) avoids EAV tables. Denormalize aggressively into the document: title, price range, rating, top seller, availability badge, image URL — everything the PLP needs, so a browse page is one fetch.
- **Browse is a facet problem**: category × brand × price × rating × availability. Store precomputed facet counts in Redis with a short TTL, and cache the rendered list response at the CDN with surrogate keys for invalidation.
- **Search in Solr/ES**: index only what search needs (title, brand, attributes, keywords), not full descriptions for large ASINs; 40–80 shards at 200k QPS; autocomplete is a separate service (see §12.5.15). Indexing is asynchronous from the catalog store — a new product appears in search within seconds, which is fine.
- **Denormalization and freshness**: price and availability change far more often than titles. Options: (a) short TTL caches (5 min) for price/offer data, which is what most sites do; (b) a "product page shell" cached for hours with a price fragment fetched fresh. I'll advocate (b) — it's a nice concrete pattern: **cache the expensive immutable parts of the page for hours and the volatile parts for seconds.**
- **Multi-seller**: a product has many offers; the PLP shows the "featured offer" (buy-box winner) computed by an offline service with price + shipping + performance + stock signals, refreshed every 5 minutes. That is a separate mini-system inside this one.

**7.4 Flash sales: load shedding as a feature**

- **Virtual queue**: for high-demand SKUs, checkout is entered through a queue that issues a token with an estimated wait. This converts a thundering herd into a controlled trickle and is the single most effective pattern.
- **Pre-warmed inventory**: materialise the SKU, the offers, the buy-box winner, and the cart templates in cache ahead of the event. Midnight should not be when the product row is first written.
- **Bot mitigation**: per-account purchase caps, per-instrument caps, device fingerprinting, CAPTCHA step-up above a velocity threshold, and reservation TTL short enough that hoarding is unprofitable.
- **Degradation ladder**: full checkout → queue → waitlist (no payment, notify on restock) → reject with 503 + `Retry-After`. Never let the system produce partial orders.
- **Sharding flash-sale SKUs** onto dedicated partitions so a 100k-buyer SKU cannot degrade browsing for the other 500M products.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **Inventory hot rows** — one SKU at 10k reservations/sec. Fixes: Redis token buckets, reservation queue, per-user caps.
2. **Search latency** — 200k QPS with a bad query plan, or index growth, or a shard rebalance during Prime Day. Fixes: pre-sized shards, no rebalances during peak, query caching for repeated navigational queries, and a fallback to a cached result set if Solr degrades.
3. **Catalog cache hit rate** — a 4 KB product document at high QPS is a lot of bytes; cache miss storms come from viral products. Fixes: surrogate-key CDN purging, stale-while-revalidate, and a "viral product" pre-warm pipeline.
4. **Order database write throughput** — orders are strongly consistent and cannot be spread freely. Fixes: shard by order id (created snowflake) rather than user, replicas for reads, and a separate analytical replica for order history queries (which is 95% of order read traffic).
5. **Payment provider latency and limits** — the longest pole in checkout. Fixes: async capture, connection pooling, multi-provider failover, circuit breakers, and never holding a DB lock across the call.
6. **Fraud model latency** — a synchronous model call at 300 ms is a third of the checkout budget. Fixes: rules-first fast path, model only on risk signals, cached scores, and a hard 300 ms timeout with a "pending review" outcome.
7. **Inventory reconciliation** — reservations, shipments, and returns constantly disagree. Requires a nightly reconciliation job and a real operations surface for breakages.
8. **Order state machine bugs** — 12 states with compensating paths; this is where real money is lost. Mitigation: a table-driven state machine with illegal transitions rejected, plus property-based tests.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Inventory service down | checkout fails cleanly with out-of-stock semantics; browse shows stale availability | **fail closed** — you must not sell what you cannot reserve |
| Payment provider down | checkout fails at authorization; carts and reservations released | alternate provider, circuit breaker |
| Search down | fall back to category browse from the catalog store, or a cached result set | browse is the fallback, not a 500 |
| Catalog store down | browse served from CDN cache (stale); product pages fail | replicas |
| Redis down | facet counts and list caches lost; checkout unaffected | recompute from the catalog store |
| Order DB primary down | no new orders for 30–60 s | replica promotion; **do not** accept orders you cannot persist |
| Fraud service down | route to manual review queue, do not approve by default | timeout policy must be explicit |
| Kafka down | order confirmation and fulfillment events delayed | orders are durable; downstream catches up |
| WMS/fulfillment down | order confirmed but not shipping | backlog queue; customer comms updated |
| Inventory reservation TTL sweeper down | stock stays "reserved" | reservations expire in the store; sweeper is for durability |
| Flash sale overload | queue, then waitlist, then reject with `Retry-After` | ladder is the feature |
| Search index corrupt | serve catalog browse; rebuild index from the catalog store asynchronously | index is derived, so it is always rebuildable |

Invariant: **never capture payment without a durable order; never sell without a reservation.** Everything else is recoverable; those two are not.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Catalog | document store / JSONB | normalized relational per category | heterogeneous attributes, whole-doc reads |
| Browse | cached, precomputed facets, surrogate-key purging | live facet counts from SQL | at 500k QPS, live counts are impossible |
| Search | dedicated Solr/ES cluster | SQL `LIKE` / relational search | typo tolerance, relevance, facets |
| Inventory | reserve-then-commit with TTL | decrement-on-order | races, oversell, and lock-holding |
| Hot-SKU control | Redis token bucket + queue + per-user caps | more DB shards | the row is one partition; sharding it does not help |
| Checkout | saga with explicit compensations | distributed transaction | third-party payment cannot join your DB transaction |
| Capture order | authorize → persist → capture | capture → persist | avoids money with no order |
| Order store | relational sharded by order id + analytical replica | document store for orders | strong consistency + relational reporting |
| Order history reads | analytical replica | primary | history is 95% of order reads and can lag |
| Address/price | snapshot on the order | FK to a live record | orders are immutable legal records |
| Oversell | allowed on low demand, forbidden on high | never oversell anywhere | conversion vs correctness, per category |
| Fraud | synchronous fast path + async review | block on a full model | latency budget |

Rejected wholesale
- **One big relational schema for catalog, inventory, orders, and payments.** Different access patterns, different consistency needs, different scaling axes.
- **A microservice per noun with synchronous chatty calls at checkout.** Latency adds up; the checkout saga should be a handful of well-defined calls.
- **Real-time inventory counts on the product page.** A hard correctness/scalability trade that is not worth it; use "in stock, 4 left" style approximations with reservation at checkout.
- **Synchronous everything.** Kafka for all post-confirmation side effects; only inventory, fraud, and payment are on the critical path.
- **Charge-on-order with no reservation.** Oversell, cancellations, and unhappy customers.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "Two buyers click Buy on the last item at the same instant. What happens?"**
Model answer: Neither of them decrements inventory directly; both attempt an atomic reservation. The reserve call moves `available → reserved` with a 10-minute TTL, and it is a single conditional write, so exactly one succeeds. The loser gets `409 out_of_stock` — and I would still show them a waitlist, because the winner frequently abandons and there is a real chance of restock. On the hot path specifically, the DB row is not involved at all: a Redis token bucket per SKU holds a small batch of units (say 100), refill in 100s from the durable store, so 10k reservations/sec becomes 100 DB writes/sec. The per-user purchase cap (five per SKU) prevents one account from clearing the bucket, which is the standard scalping move. After payment succeeds the reservation commits to a sale; if payment fails or the TTL lapses, a sweeper returns the units. And at the very bottom, the durable store still has a unique constraint and an `available >= requested` check, so even a bug in all of the above produces a failed reservation rather than an oversold order.

**Q2. "Payment charged, order write failed. Now what?"**
Model answer: That ordering should not be possible, because I never capture before the order is durable. I authorize (reserving funds without moving them), persist the order, then capture. So the real failure to handle is authorize-then-persist-fail, and the compensation is a **void** of the authorization plus releasing the inventory reservation. Capturing first would mean money with no order, which is a refund and a support ticket; my worst case becomes a void, which is free. I still handle the residual: capture fails after the order is confirmed, which goes to a retry queue and I deliberately do not auto-cancel a customer who already has a confirmation email. And the case where the provider's response to my capture is ambiguous goes to a daily reconciliation against the settlement file, keyed by idempotency token, so nothing is silently mismatched. All of it is idempotent — keys derived from `orderId + step` — because mobile clients retry and a duplicate capture is a real event, not a hypothetical. Money lives in an append-only double-entry ledger; refunds are compensating entries.

**Q3. "How do you keep a 500M-product catalog fast?"**
Model answer: Split it by access pattern, because the catalog has three different problems wearing one name. The product page is a read-whole of a heterogeneous document, so products are documents with denormalized price range, rating, top seller, and availability badge — one fetch renders the page. Browse is a facet-count problem, so facet counts are precomputed into Redis with short TTLs and the list responses are cached at the CDN with surrogate keys for targeted invalidation. Search is a relevance problem, so it lives in Solr with only the searchable fields indexed, async from the catalog store, with query caching for the navigational queries that dominate. The trick that keeps all three fast is splitting the page into a highly cacheable shell and a volatile fragment: title, images, description, and reviews cached for hours; price, stock, and delivery estimate cached for seconds and fetched as a separate call. That way a viral product produces one origin read and then almost nothing. And for genuine viral events I pre-warm the product and its buy-box offer into the edge, because the miss storm is the whole problem.

**Q4. "A flash sale: 100k people, 500 units. Describe the system."**
Model answer: The queue is the design. All 100k buyers enter a virtual queue that issues a token with an estimated wait, and only a bounded number of checkout sessions proceed at once — a few hundred — so the hot SKU never sees 100k concurrent writers. Those sessions reserve from a Redis token bucket holding the 500 units, which means zero database writes during the sale; the durable store is refilled in batches afterward. Each account is capped at a small number of units with per-instrument and device limits, and a step-up challenge triggers on velocity, so bots cannot drain the bucket. I pre-warm the SKU, the buy-box offer, and the cart template into cache beforehand, so midnight is not when the row is first written. If the queue outruns stock I offer a waitlist rather than failing, because the winner will often abandon and there will be restock. If the system genuinely cannot keep up, the ladder is queue → waitlist → 503 with `Retry-After`, and crucially the flash-sale SKU sits on a dedicated partition so none of this touches browsing for the other 500M products. And I would never oversell to smooth the graph: a refund storm plus a review problem costs more than the extra sales.

**Q5. "How do you keep the order state machine from becoming a bug farm?"**
Model answer: Make illegal transitions impossible rather than merely discouraged. One explicit table of states and the permitted transitions between them, consulted by a single state-machine function, so a transition that is not in the table throws in development and is rejected in production. Every transition appends an immutable row to `order_events` with from-state, to-state, reason, actor, and timestamp, which gives you a reconstructable journal — that journal is what you read when an order is in an impossible state, and it is the difference between five minutes and five hours of debugging. All transitions are idempotent on `orderId + targetState`, because at-least-once delivery into the saga means retries are guaranteed. Compensations are first-class transitions in the same table, not ad-hoc code, so the set of things that can undo each other is enumerable. And I property-test the whole thing: generate random sequences of concurrent API calls against a saga and assert the invariants — never two live orders for one reservation, never captured without a persisted order, every order reaches a terminal state or has a pending compensation.

**Q6. "Your search is returning 800 ms p99 during peak. What do you do?"**
Model answer: First, identify whether it is a real regression or the load profile changing — an unexpected query shape or a segment merge running during peak is the usual culprit, and I would check the slow-query log before touching capacity. Then the cheap fixes: cache the repeated navigational queries, which are the majority of search traffic on any commerce site, and cap pagination depth so nobody asks for page 500. If it is genuine load, 200k QPS on a 40–80 shard cluster is in range, so I would look for a hot shard from an unanalyzed keyword, which is extremely common with a single popular term, and rebalance by adding routing replicas for that term. I would also confirm the index is not mid-rebuild, because reindexing during peak is a self-inflicted outage and I would make reindex-and-swap a blue/green operation. If search is still unhealthy I degrade rather than fail: fall back to category browse served from the catalog store's cached facets, which is a worse experience but a working site. Separately I would look at whether the latency is in the service or in the network, since a p50 of 80 ms with a p99 of 800 ms is almost always a tail caused by a few slow shards or GC pauses, not uniform slowness.

---

## 12.5.14 Payment system

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design a payment system. Multiple providers, cards and wallets, refunds and chargebacks, international currencies, and it must never lose or double-charge money."

The score is in: **double-entry bookkeeping**, **exactly-once via idempotency keys**, **PCI scope reduction via tokenization**, **the saga between authorization/capture/refund**, and **reconciliation against provider settlement files**.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Authorize, capture, void, refund (full and partial), credit.
- F2. Multiple providers (Stripe, Adyen, bank acquirer) with failover.
- F3. Multiple methods: cards, wallets, bank transfer, BNPL.
- F4. Currencies with FX quote, capture, and settlement.
- F5. 3DS/SCA step-up, AVS/CVV checks, tokenized cards, network tokens.
- F6. Recurring payments, saved payment methods, mandates.
- F7. Refunds, partial refunds, chargeback/dispute intake and representation.
- F8. Payouts to sellers/merchants.

Non-functional
- N1. **Never lose money, never double-charge.** RPO = 0 on the ledger.
- N2. Authorization latency: **< 2 s** p99 including provider call and 3DS.
- N3. Availability 99.99% for authorization; the ledger must survive provider failure.
- N4. Idempotent: replaying any request with the same key produces the same result.
- N5. Auditability: every cent traceable, immutable history, exportable for finance.
- N6. Multi-region active-active with a **single writer for the ledger** or a rigorously serialized account balance.
- N7. Compliance: PCI-DSS SAQ-A by tokenizing at the edge, PSD2/SCA in EU, PCI DSS 4.0, regional data residency.

---

**3. Estimation**

Volume
- A large processor: 300M transactions/year, peak 5k TPS (Black Friday/Cyber Monday can hit 20–30k TPS).
- 300M × 600 B = **180 GB/year** of transaction records. Volume is not the problem; **correctness is**.

Storage
- Ledger entries: ~200 B each, two entries per transaction → 600M rows/year = **120 GB/year**. Append-only.
- Card vault records: ~150 B × 100M tokens = **15 GB**. Small, heavily encrypted, heavily audited.
- Reconciliation records: one row per provider settlement line, 365 days retained.

Bandwidth
- 5k TPS × 2 KB (provider request/response, 3DS payloads) = **10 MB/s** out, similar in. Not a scale problem.
- **3DS redirect flows** involve the customer's browser leaving to the issuer and back — the flow is not a clean API call and has a 5–15 minute window; the state machine must survive it.

Arithmetic to state
- "Two entries per transaction, both immutable, and balances are derived. That single rule — append-only double entry, no mutable balances — is what makes the system auditable and reconstructable. Everything else in the design is plumbing around it."

---

**4. API design**

```
POST /v1/payments/authorizations
{ "orderId":"o_77", "amount": 4999, "currency":"USD",
  "paymentMethodToken":"pm_tok_9f3a",     // NOT a PAN
  "idempotencyKey":"uuid", "captureMethod":"manual",
  "threeDs": { "required": true, "returnUrl":"https://..." },
  "metadata": {...} }
-> 201 { "authorizationId":"auth_1", "state":"requires_action",
         "clientSecret":"pi_...",           // for SDK-side 3DS
         "amount":4999, "currency":"USD", "expiresAt":"..." }
     (or 201 state="authorized" when no challenge is required)
```

```
POST /v1/authorizations/{id}/capture   { "amount": 4999, "idempotencyKey":"uuid" }
-> 200 { "captureId":"cap_1", "state":"captured", "providerTxnId":"...", "netAmount": 4700 }
POST /v1/authorizations/{id}/void
POST /v1/captures/{id}/refunds        { "amount": 1200, "reason":"requested_by_customer", "idempotencyKey":"..." }
-> 201 { "refundId":"rf_1", "state":"pending" }
GET  /v1/payments/{id}                 -> full state machine view + ledger entries
POST /v1/payment-methods               -> creates a token via the SDK; server never sees a PAN
POST /v1/3ds/callbacks                 (issuer callback, signed, idempotent)
POST /v1/disputes                      (webhook intake from provider)
```

States, made explicit
```
INTENT -> REQUIRES_ACTION -> AUTHORIZED -> CAPTURED -> (PARTIALLY_REFUNDED) -> REFUNDED
                     \-> DECLINED
                     \-> EXPIRED (auth validity window passed)
                     \-> FAILED (provider/technical)
any state -> REVERSED (void) or DISPUTED (chargeback, independent of refund)
```
Error semantics
| Case | Response | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Idempotency key replay | **200/201 with the original response** | never re-charge |
| Card declined | 402 with `declineCode` (`insufficient_funds`, `do_not_honor`) | map codes; do not retry hard declines |
| Provider timeout, unknown outcome | 202 `pending` + reconcile | **never** retry blindly with a new key |
| Partial capture not supported | 422 | validate before calling |
| 3DS abandoned | authorization expires; release inventory | the saga handles it |
| Refund exceeds remaining | 409 | enforce server-side |
| Currency mismatch | 422 | quote is per-currency |
| Chargeback opened | webhook → `DISPUTED`, evidence deadline tracked | never auto-refund a dispute |

---

**5. Data model**

```
ledger_entries                -- APPEND-ONLY, IMMUTABLE, double-entry
  entry_id        BIGINT PRIMARY KEY     -- snowflake
  txn_id          UUID                   -- groups the entries of one transaction
  account         TEXT                    -- e.g. "merchant:9f3c", "psp:stripe:clearing",
                                         -- "customer:credit_card:9f3a", "fx:USD:EUR"
  direction       ENUM(debit, credit)
  amount_minor    BIGINT                 -- ALWAYS minor units. NEVER a float.
  currency        CHAR(3)
  entry_type      ENUM(authorization, capture, refund, fee, chargeback, fx, payout, reversal)
  created_at      TIMESTAMPTZ
  idempotency_key UUID
  INDEX (txn_id)
  INDEX (account, created_at)
```
```
accounts
  account, type, currency, created_at
  -- balances are NOT stored; they are a projection over ledger_entries
```
```
payment_transactions         -- state machine projection
  payment_id, order_id, merchant_id, state, amount, currency,
  authorization_id, capture_id, provider, provider_state,
  created_at, updated_at, version
  UNIQUE (merchant_id, idempotency_key)
```
```
payment_methods              -- tokenized only
  pm_id, customer_id, provider, network_token, brand, last4, exp_month, exp_year,
  vault_key_id, default_for_shipping
```
```
provider_translations        -- the reconciliation map
  txn_id, provider, provider_txn_id, raw_response JSONB, settled_at, match_state
```
```
reconciliation_runs
  run_id, provider, period, expected_settlements, matched, unmatched, discrepancy_cents
```
```
disputes
  dispute_id, payment_id, provider_dispute_id, reason_code, amount,
  evidence_deadline, status, evidence JSONB
```

The `UNIQUE (merchant_id, idempotency_key)` constraint is the anti-double-charge guarantee at the database level — the same trick as the e-commerce order. Amounts in **minor units as integers** (no floats, ever) is the other.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Merchant service ---> Payment Orchestrator (the state machine)
                        |
     +------------------+------------------+
     |                  |                  |
  Ledger (append-   Provider        Risk/3DS
  only, strong)     Adapters        (rules, then model)
                        |                  |
                   Stripe/Adyen/      Issuer (browser redirect)
                   Acquirer/PSP       via client SDK
                        |
     +------------------+------------------+
     |                  |                  |
  Webhooks (signed,   Reconciliation    Payouts / Settlement
  idempotent)         (settlement files)

Tokenization: Card Data -> Provider-hosted JS/SDK iFrame / Network token
               -> PCI SAQ-A. The server NEVER sees a PAN.
```

Every state change appends to the ledger and publishes to Kafka; projections (transaction view, balances) are rebuilt from the ledger. Webhooks are idempotent and every provider call carries an idempotency key.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Double-entry bookkeeping and derived balances**

- Every transaction creates **balanced entries** across accounts: a capture debits `merchant_receivable` and credits `psp_clearing` (or the reverse, depending on whose perspective you normalize to); a refund creates the exact inverse; fees are their own transaction type with their own entries; chargebacks are a separate transaction type, never a mutation of the original.
- Invariant enforced by construction: `SUM(debits) == SUM(credits)` per `txn_id`. Assert it in the database with a deferred constraint or a per-transaction check, and alert on any imbalance — this is the single most valuable alert in a payments system.
- **Balances are a projection.** `SUM(amount * direction) GROUP BY account` over recent entries, with periodic snapshots so the projection can be rebuilt. Never a mutable `balance` column that can drift, because drift is invisible until an audit.
- **Amounts in minor units, integers, with the currency on the entry.** A float anywhere in a money path is a defect.
- **FX** is a transaction type, not arithmetic: a conversion creates paired entries in both currencies plus an FX account difference, so the rates used are recorded and auditable rather than recomputed later.
- **Reversal, never deletion.** There is no `DELETE` on the ledger; a mistake is corrected by a compensating transaction. Retention and legal-hold rules follow from this.

**7.2 Idempotency: the mechanism that prevents double-charging**

- The merchant supplies an `idempotencyKey` per logical intent. The API stores `(merchant_id, idempotency_key) -> request_fingerprint, response_snapshot` with a 24h+ TTL, enforced by a unique index.
- On replay with the **same** key and the **same** fingerprint → return the stored response, do not call the provider. On replay with the same key but a **different** fingerprint → `409 idempotency_conflict`, because that is a client bug that would otherwise produce a surprise charge.
- The key is propagated to the provider as *their* idempotency key, so a retry after an ambiguous timeout is collapsed provider-side too. This is the belt-and-braces that makes "unknown outcome" survivable.
- **Unknown outcomes are the hard case.** If the provider call times out, you do not know whether the money moved. Correct handling: return `202 pending`, record the attempt, and let reconciliation with the provider's settlement file determine the outcome. Never retry with a fresh key — that is how double charges happen.
- Concurrency: two simultaneous requests with the same key must serialize on the unique index (one inserts, the other reads the winner's response or waits), which is why this is a database constraint and not an application check.

**7.3 Reconciliation, chargebacks, and the parts that are not APIs**

- **Reconciliation is a first-class subsystem**, not a report. Providers send daily settlement files listing what they actually moved. The job matches them against internal `provider_translations` by provider transaction id and amount, then classifies each as matched, unmatched-internal (provider has it, we do not), unmatched-external (we have it, they do not), or amount-mismatched. Any discrepancy creates a case in a reconciliation work queue with an owner. **A payments system without daily reconciliation is a system that discovers its errors months later.**
- **Chargebacks** are a separate machine: intake webhook → `DISPUTED` state → evidence deadline tracked as a first-class field with escalation → evidence submission (must be idempotent) → resolution, which may be a debit or a win. Never auto-refund a dispute; the representment flow often wins, and auto-refunding destroys the reason merchants dispute at all.
- **3DS/SCA** is a state machine spanning an out-of-band browser redirect with a 5–15 minute window. The authorization sits in `REQUIRES_ACTION`, an idempotent callback endpoint resolves it, and an expiry sweeper releases inventory for abandoned challenges. The issuer's callback must be signature-verified *and* idempotent, because issuers retry.
