- **Provider failover**: adapters behind a common interface, with health-based routing and per-provider circuit breakers. Routing by observed success rate, not config, because providers fail regionally.
- **Payouts/settlement** to merchants: a separate batch pipeline with its own ledger entries, its own reconciliation against bank statements, and its own SLA. Do not reuse the customer-facing payment path for money leaving the system.

**7.4 PCI scope, tokenization, and what to never build**

- **Never handle a PAN** in your systems. Card data goes to a provider-hosted iFrame or a mobile SDK; what returns is a token. Result: PCI-DSS **SAQ-A** (eligible self-assessment) instead of SAQ-D, which is a difference of months of compliance work.
- **Vault only tokens**, encrypted with a KMS-managed key, with the key id stored alongside so rotation is possible. Store brand, last4, and expiry for display; those are permitted.
- **Network tokens** (Apple Pay / Google Pay push-provisioned tokens) are better than your own vault entries: the device manages them and they rotate.
- **Never** log a PAN, a CVV, a full track data equivalent, or even a partially masked-plus-something identifier. Log the token id and the last4 only. This is a policy with automated enforcement (log scrubbing in the pipeline) rather than a code-review promise.
- **Security controls that belong in the design**: field-level encryption, separate keys per environment, HSM/KMS for key operations, key rotation with dual-read, strict IAM with no long-lived credentials, full audit of vault access (who decrypted which token, when), and PII/token separation so the vault can be a separately-audited system.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **Provider rate limits and latency** — the dominant constraint, not your own capacity. Fixes: multiple providers, per-provider concurrency caps, adaptive routing on observed latency and success, and a queue with a hard admission limit.
2. **3DS webhooks** arrive in unpredictable bursts and are retried by issuers; idempotent endpoints plus a high ingestion headroom.
3. **Ledger write throughput** — append-only, strongly consistent, ~2× the transaction rate. Fix: partition by `txn_id` or account, sequence per partition, and consider a per-tenant serialization model. Throughput of 20k TPS is achievable with careful partitioning; the hard part is the balance invariant.
4. **Balance projections** — a `SUM` over ever-growing entries. Fix: incremental projection in a materialized view plus periodic snapshots and rebuild tooling.
5. **Reconciliation at volume** — matching 300M/year against settlement files is a batch problem; needs partitioning by provider and day, and must be idempotent and resumable.
6. **Fraud/risk latency** inside the authorization budget. Fix: rules first, model only on risk, cached scores, hard timeout with an explicit policy.
7. **Dispute deadlines** — an operational SLA that requires reliable alerting, not just storage.
8. **Multi-region active-active with one ledger writer** — cross-region latency on every authorization, or a rigorously serialized account. This is the hardest scaling topology problem here and the honest answer is usually a single home region per merchant with read replicas elsewhere.
9. **Key management and vault audit volume** — the compliance pipeline is itself a distributed system.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Provider outage | route to an alternate provider; if none, reject with `503` | health-based routing; queued retries for authorizations only |
| Provider timeout with unknown outcome | `202 pending`; **do not retry with a new key** | reconciliation determines the real outcome |
| Webhook storm or duplicates | idempotent endpoints absorb it | dedupe by provider event id |
| 3DS callback lost | authorization stays `REQUIRES_ACTION` until expiry | active poll of the authorization status; expiry releases inventory |
| Ledger unavailable | **refuse to authorize** | fail closed — never authorize money you cannot record |
| Balance projection lag | stale balance display; authorizations still use the authoritative reserve path | projection catches up; alert on lag |
| Reconciliation finds a mismatch | case created, merchant funds held | work queue with an owner and an SLA |
| Vault/keys unavailable | cannot de-tokenize; new payment methods fail, existing authorizations still work | KMS failover; tokenization lets you fail this narrowly |
| Risk service down | timeout policy: rules-only fast path or pending-review | never approve by default |
| FX rate service down | quote uses the last known rate with an explicit staleness bound | block above a staleness threshold |
| Region failure | route to the home region | RPO 0 if the ledger replicated synchronously; RTO minutes |
| Duplicate charge reported | trace by `idempotency_key`; almost always a client bug or a retry with a new key | refund as a compensating entry; feed the pattern into client SDK fixes |
| Chargeback deadline missed | merchant loses automatically | escalation alerts well before the deadline |

Invariant: **money is only ever moved by appending balanced ledger entries.** There is no other path in the system.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Card handling | provider tokenization, SAQ-A | storing PANs (SAQ-D) | months of compliance work eliminated |
| Ledger | append-only double-entry, derived balances | mutable balance columns | auditability and no drift |
| Corrections | compensating transactions | delete/update | history is the product |
| Idempotency | unique `(merchant, key)` + provider key | application check only | must be enforced at the DB |
| Unknown outcomes | `pending` + reconciliation | blind retry | blind retry is the double-charge bug |
| Amounts | integer minor units + currency | floats, or decimals without units | float in money is a defect |
| Providers | multi-adapter with health routing | single provider | availability and negotiating position |
| Failover mode | reject/queue rather than degrade silently | authorize and reconcile later | you cannot authorize money you cannot record |
| Ledger topology | single home region per merchant, replicas | active-active with distributed ledger | ordering and the balance invariant are not worth the latency |
| 3DS | state machine with expiry | blocking the request | the browser redirect takes minutes |
| Dispute handling | representment workflow | auto-refund | auto-refunding removes the merchant's ability to win |
| Reconciliation | daily automated matching + work queue | manual month-end | errors surface while they are still fixable |
| Reconciliation store | separate analytic store | the primary DB | batch scans must not touch the live path |

Rejected wholesale
- **A single "payments" table with a status column and an amount.** No history, no audit, no reconciliation, no multi-currency.
- **Storing a "last4 + bin" as your own card record and calling it tokenization.** It is not; the token must be uninterpretable to you.
- **Retrying a timed-out capture with a new idempotency key.** This is how a payments system loses trust.
- **Authorizing and capturing in the same synchronous call with no intermediate persistence.** A crash between them leaves an unknown state everywhere.
- **Letting the payment service call the merchant's order service synchronously for the order amount.** Amount must be passed in and validated against a signed quote, not re-fetched mid-charge.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "Explain double-entry to me."**
Model answer: Every movement of value is expressed as a balanced set of entries, and balances are never stored — they are derived. A capture of $49.99 is two entries: debit `merchant_receivable`, credit `psp_clearing`, both tagged with the same transaction id, both in integer minor units with the currency attached. A refund is the exact inverse as a *new* transaction, never an edit. Fees are their own transaction type with their own paired entries. FX is a transaction, not arithmetic: converting USD to EUR produces paired entries in both currencies plus an FX difference account, so the rate used is recorded and auditable. The invariant is that debits equal credits for every transaction id, and I enforce that with a database constraint plus an alert, because that single check catches essentially every class of bug in a payments system. Nothing is ever deleted or mutated — a mistake is corrected by a compensating entry. That is what makes the system reconstructable: given the ledger, you can replay every balance at any point in time, which is exactly what finance, auditors, and dispute handlers need.

**Q2. "A capture times out and you don't know if it went through. What now?"**
Model answer: The rule is: never retry with a new idempotency key, because that is precisely how a double charge happens. Correct handling has four steps. First, persist the attempt locally with the idempotency key *before* calling the provider, so we have a durable record that we asked. Second, return `202 pending` to the merchant — the caller must know it is not failed, because a merchant that retries on a 5xx will create the duplicate. Third, propagate the same idempotency key to the provider on any retry, so if the provider did process it, their side collapses the duplicate; this is why provider-native idempotency is the single most important thing to use correctly. Fourth, let reconciliation settle it: the provider's daily settlement file is matched against our `provider_translations` table, and the attempt is resolved to succeeded or failed from the settlement data rather than from a guess. In the meantime the merchant's inventory reservation stays alive because the saga is in a pending state with a TTL, and if it expires before resolution we mark the payment as needing manual review rather than silently refunding a charge that may have succeeded. And I would surface this state explicitly in the API, because the merchant's correct behavior depends on it.

**Q3. "How do you keep PCI scope small?"**
Model answer: By never seeing a card number. Card data goes from the browser or the app straight into a provider-hosted iFrame or mobile SDK, and what comes back is a token that is meaningless outside that provider. So I store the token, the brand, the last four, and the expiry — I store no PAN, no CVV, no track data — which puts the system in PCI-DSS SAQ-A instead of SAQ-D. The difference is months of compliance work and a materially smaller audit surface. I go further and prefer network tokens where available: Apple Pay and Google Pay push-provisioned tokens are managed by the device, rotate on their own, and are bound to the device, so there is nothing long-lived for me to protect. The vault that does hold tokens is a separate, narrowly-permuted system with field-level encryption under a KMS-managed key, the key id stored alongside so keys can be rotated with dual-read, and every decryption attempt written to an audit log — who decrypted which token, when, from where. And I enforce "never log a PAN" mechanically with log scrubbing in the pipeline rather than by asking reviewers to remember, because that rule is violated by accident, not on purpose.

**Q4. "A dispute arrives with an evidence deadline in 48 hours. What does your system do?"**
Model answer: It treats the deadline as a first-class field with alerting, because missing it is an automatic loss and no engineering failure is more painful than that. The intake is a signed provider webhook that moves the payment to `DISPUTED` — note that a dispute is not a refund, so it does not touch the refund state machine — and records the reason code, amount, and evidence deadline. From there a case is created with an owner, and escalation alerts fire at 48, 24, and 4 hours out. Evidence submission is itself idempotent, since providers retry and a duplicate submission can be rejected. The rule I would insist on is never auto-refunding a dispute: representment often wins — the card network's rules require evidence, not acceptance — and auto-refunding means the merchant permanently loses the 1–3% dispute revenue and any incentive to contest fraudulent claims. There is a legitimate exception for fraud-classified claims above a value threshold, where the loss is certain and the operational cost of fighting is not worth it, and that exception is a policy decision with a documented threshold rather than a code path someone improvised. Finally the dispute outcome writes a `chargeback` transaction type to the ledger, so the money side and the operational side are both closed out and reconciled.

**Q5. "Multiple providers. How do you route?"**
Model answer: By observed performance, not configuration. Each provider adapter exposes health, p99 latency, success rate by error class, and current rate-limit headroom, and the orchestrator routes on that with per-provider concurrency caps. That matters because providers fail regionally and for specific reasons — one has an outage in a market while being fine elsewhere, one degrades under a specific card type, one starts returning soft declines — and static config cannot express any of it. Failover has to be shaped by error class too: a card decline is not a provider failure and must never trigger a retry elsewhere, because retrying a decline at a second provider produces duplicate authorizations and confusing merchant-visible behavior. Only technical errors, timeouts with unknown outcome, and 5xx route to an alternate. For unknown outcomes the alternate gets the *same* idempotency key, so if the first provider did process it, the chain does not double-charge. And I cap the chain at two or three attempts, because a deep retry chain across providers multiplies latency past the 2-second authorization budget for very little availability gain — beyond a point the correct answer is to decline and tell the merchant to retry, not to keep trying.

**Q6. "Where would a bug actually lose me money?"**
Model answer: In five places, in this order. One, an ambiguous capture timeout followed by a retry with a fresh idempotency key — a direct double charge, and it is entirely a client or integration error rather than a database problem. Two, capture-before-persist ordering, so a crash leaves money with no order. Three, refund accounting that mutates instead of compensating, so the ledger no longer reconciles and you only discover it at month-end. Four, float arithmetic or a currency mismatch, which produces a rounding leak that is invisible until it is large. Five, reconciliation not running, so mismatches accumulate for months. The common thread is that all five are cases where the system made an assumption about something it had not verified. The defences are therefore all verification mechanisms rather than clever logic: idempotency enforced by a unique index, authorize-persist-capture ordering, append-only ledger with a per-transaction balance constraint, integer minor units, and daily reconciliation with a work queue. If I were reviewing a payments system I would want exactly three dashboards — unknown outcomes by age, unmatched reconciliation lines by age, and dispute deadlines outstanding — and I would treat any of them being non-empty as an incident.

---

## 12.5.15 Search autocomplete / typeahead

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design the search autocomplete bar. Every keystroke gets a request. It's the most-used feature on the page and it has to feel instant."

The score is in: this is a **read-heavy, low-latency, high-QPS system with a tiny latency budget and enormous cache leverage**. Interviewers look for (a) why you trim at the edge rather than generating suggestions, (b) the trie / finite-state transducer in-memory approach versus a database lookup, (c) prefix + popularity ranking, (d) sharding a 500M-entry dictionary, and (e) personalization and typo tolerance without destroying the cache hit rate.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Suggestions for prefix input: queries, entities, categories, completions.
- F2. Typo tolerance ("gogle" → "google").
- F3. Trending boost that decays over hours.
- F4. Personalization (recent searches, language, locale).
- F5. Category/entity icons and types in the response.
- F6. Click-through logging to improve ranking.
- F7. Suggestion of the full query with highlighted prefix.

Non-functional
- N1. **< 50 ms p99** end-to-end from keystroke to render.
- N2. At 50M DAU and ~6 keystrokes per session → **~3.5M requests/sec peak**. This is the dominant number.
- N3. Cache hit rate > 90% — at 3.5k QPS per node, this is where the cost is won or lost.
- N4. Suggestions must be available **before the user finishes typing**, so requests must be cancellable/debounced on the client.
- N5. Availability: autocomplete failure degrades to a plain search box, silently.
- N6. Dictionary updates (new entities, trending terms) visible within minutes.

---

**3. Estimation**

Request volume
- 50M DAU × 1 search session × 6 prefix requests = 300M requests/day = **3.5k QPS average**, ~14k QPS peak. At 200 B response that's 3.5 MB/s — trivial bandwidth, brutal QPS.
- If you served every prefix from an origin database you would need ~14k QPS × 500 reads each = **7M reads/sec** on an index. Infeasible. Hence the design.

Dictionary size
- 500M unique queries, top-level queries ~10B. Prefix entries (distinct prefixes across the corpus) — for 500M queries averaging 15 chars, distinct prefixes ≈ **3–5 billion** if precomputed. You do *not* precompute them.
- Instead: hold the 10–50M most valuable queries **in memory** as a trie, and everything else in a search backend queried with prefix + popularity filters.
- Per-node memory: a compact trie over 50M queries ≈ **1–2 GB** across all shards (roughly 20–40 bytes per query after prefix compression). That fits.

Arithmetic to state
- "10B queries but only 50M are worth autocompleting; the long tail contributes almost no suggestion value. So I trim the dictionary, and 50M queries in a compact trie is ~1.5 GB, which is 1 GB per shard across a few hundred nodes. That is the whole trick: trim, compress, put in RAM, cache at the edge."

---

**4. API design**

```
GET /suggest?q=gol&limit=10&locale=en-US&userTier=logged_in
-> 200
{ "prefix": "gol",
  "suggestions": [
    { "text":"google", "type":"QUERY", "highlightRanges":[[0,3]], "score": 982341,
      "trend": 1.8, "isPersonalized": false },
    { "text":"google maps", "type":"QUERY", "score": 512003 },
    { "text":"Golden State Warriors vs Celtics", "type":"ENTITY", "entityId":"e_88", "iconUrl":"..." }
  ],
  "tookMs": 4, "dictVersion": "2026-10-03-14:20" }
```
```
POST /suggest/log     { "q":"gol", "suggestion":"google", "position":0, "sessionId":"...", "ts":"..." }
-> 202      (batched, sampled; this is the training signal)
POST /suggest/sync    { "recentSearches":["...","..."], "locale":"en-US" }
-> 200
```
Client contract, which is part of the API:
- Debounce 30–50 ms; cancel in-flight requests with `AbortController` (stale responses must never render).
- Request only on prefix length ≥ 2 and only for prefixes not in a client-side LRU of recent responses.
- Prefetch the next likely prefix from the returned suggestion list.

Error behavior
| Case | Behavior |
|---|---|
| Suggestion service down | **silent fallback to plain search**; never block typing |
| Empty/1-char prefix | 200 with empty suggestions, no cache entry |
| Rate limit (abuse/bots) | 429 with a short TTL so bots don't fill the cache |
| Unknown locale | fall back to the global dictionary |
| Stale dict version | serve stale for up to 1h; trending boost is already approximate |

---

**5. Data model**

```
queries
  query TEXT PRIMARY KEY
  popularity BIGINT          -- exponentially decayed search count
  click_count BIGINT
  intent ENUM(nav, info, transactional)
  locale, country
  first_seen, last_seen
```
```
trends
  query TEXT, hour TIMESTAMPTZ, score DOUBLE PRECISION     -- 1-min buckets for trending
```
```
click_logs            -- append-only, columnar, 30-90 day TTL
  query_prefix, suggestion, position, ts, session_hash, country, device_class
```
```
dictionary_snapshot   -- versioned artifact per locale, published to all shards
  version, locale, generated_at, trie_blob_uri, size_bytes, checksum
```
```
personalization       -- recent searches per user, Redis, TTL 30 days
  recent:{userId} -> LIST(20) of queries
```

Access patterns
| Pattern | Served by |
|---|---|
| `GET /suggest` | edge cache → in-process trie → search backend |
| Trending boost | Redis sorted set per prefix, rebuilt every minute |
| Click logging | Kafka → columnar sink; training job updates `queries.popularity` |
| Dictionary update | snapshot artifact → push to all shards → hot-swap |
| Personalization | Redis recent searches, merged client-side or server-side |

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Browser (debounce 40ms, abort stale, client LRU)
  |
  v
CDN / Edge PoP cache  (key: prefix+locale+tier; TTL 60-300s; hit rate ~70-85%)
  | miss
  v
Suggest Service (2000+ nodes, no shared state, autoscale on QPS)
  |
  +-- in-process compact TRIE over the top ~50M queries   (~1-2 GB, sharded by hash(prefix))
  |      returns candidates for the prefix
  |
  +-- rank:  score = w1*popularity + w2*trending + w3*personalization + w4*prefixAffinity
  |      trending from Redis (1-min buckets), personalization from recent searches
  |
  +-- if < limit candidates -> long-tail search backend (prefix query + top-N by popularity)
  |      this happens for maybe 5% of requests
  |
  +-- return top-k with highlights
  |
Click logs -> Kafka -> popularity/trending jobs -> trie snapshot -> push to all nodes
```

Diagram this as: edge cache → stateless suggest nodes → (trie | long-tail backend) with a Kafka-fed dictionary pipeline feeding the trie.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Trie in memory, long tail in the backend, cache at the edge**

- **Trie (or FST) for the head.** A compact trie over 50M queries gives prefix lookup in O(prefix length) with no index seeks and no database. Prefix compression (radix tree) cuts memory substantially versus a naive 26-way trie. An FST can be loaded from a memory-mapped snapshot and swapped atomically, so a dictionary update is a file replacement, not a restart.
- **Sharding by prefix**: shard on `hash(prefix[0..2])` so all queries sharing a prefix live on the same node — otherwise a lookup becomes a scatter-gather across every shard, which defeats the purpose of having the data in RAM.
- **Long tail in a search backend** (Solr/Elasticsearch prefix query with a popularity filter, or a dedicated key-value store ordered for prefix scan). Only ~5% of prefixes need it.
- **Edge cache is the real lever.** 70–85% of requests are for prefixes that were requested recently (people type the same things), and the responses are near-identical for everyone. Cache at the PoP with a 60–300 s TTL keyed by `prefix + locale + tier`. Personalization must **not** be in the cached key if you want a high hit rate — instead personalize in the service after the cache, or personalize only the top-3 reorder, which is cheap and keeps the cache shared.
- **Trim aggressively.** Top 50M queries is 99%+ of suggestion value. Add newly rising queries from the trending pipeline; retire queries whose decayed popularity falls below a threshold. This is a weekly job, and its output is a versioned artifact.

**7.2 Ranking: popularity, trend, affinity**

```
score = 0.55*log1p(decayedPopularity)
      + 0.25*log1p(trendScore)            -- 1-min buckets, 6h half-life
      + 0.15*prefixAffinity              -- length & word-boundary match
      + 0.05*personalization             -- recent searches, exact matches boosted
      + 0.05*intentPrior                 -- 'google' is navigational, boost it
```
- **Decayed popularity** matters more than raw counts: a query that was huge two years ago should not outrank today's trend. Decay with a half-life of days-to-weeks and recompute in a batch job.
- **Trending** is a separate Redis sorted set per prefix, updated every minute from a Kafka stream. It is deliberately fast-moving and deliberately short-lived; that is why trending can be served approximately.
- **Intent priors** are a cheap, large win: navigational queries ("google", "amazon") should rank above informational ones ("google gravity"), and intent is inferable from CTR and query structure.
- **Length and word-boundary affinity**: matching a full word beats matching mid-word; a completed-word prefix should be boosted.
- Ranking runs in microseconds per candidate in-process; do not call a model service in this path.

**7.3 Latency engineering: the 50 ms budget**

- **Client side**: debounce 30–50 ms, abort superseded requests, cache recent prefix→response pairs in an LRU, and prefill from history so the common case needs no network at all.
- **Edge**: an in-memory cache in the PoP plus a CDN lookup; a hit is ~5–10 ms total.
- **Service**: keep the working set in RAM; avoid serialization overhead (a hand-rolled binary response beats JSON); avoid GC pauses with a low allocation rate; and **never do I/O on the trie path**.
- **Long tail** is the latency risk. Hard budget of ~15 ms with a timeout, and if it misses, return what the trie found rather than failing — a partial list renders instantly and the user is already typing the next character.
- **Early termination**: stop scanning trie branches once you have `limit × 3` high-scoring candidates. A naive full traversal is O(matches) and can be milliseconds.
- **Precompute common prefix expansions**: for prefixes shorter than 3 characters, precompute the top suggestions into a static map — those are trivially cacheable and cover a huge share of keystrokes.
- **Never let a slow request delay the UI**: the box always accepts input; suggestions are an enhancement.

**7.4 Typos, safety, and abuse**

- **Typo tolerance**: an edit-distance or phonetic pre-filter over a small set of high-traffic terms ("gogle", "amzon", "whtsup"), never a full fuzzy scan of 500M queries. For the long tail, the search backend handles it. Keep the tolerated distance at 1 for short prefixes and 2 for prefixes ≥ 5 characters.
- **Adult/illegal content filtering**: filter at the source (dictionary curation) *and* on the response (a cheap trie of blocked prefixes, O(prefix length)), because a dictionary pulled from live logs will eventually contain anything.
- **PII in recent searches**: user recent-search history is sensitive; store hashed or short-TTL and never echo it to other users; provide a delete.
- **Bot abuse**: bots typing prefixes to fill the edge cache with junk (cache pollution) is a real attack. Defenses: rate limit by IP/device, do not cache responses above a certain anomaly score, and monitor cache fill rate by prefix entropy.
- **Trending abuse**: coordinated manipulation of trending terms. Mitigate with velocity caps, source diversity requirements, and a manual review queue for the top-trending list.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **QPS, before anything else.** 14k peak RPS is 2,000+ nodes. The lever is the edge cache; without it you are paying for a cache miss rate you do not need to pay.
2. **Trie memory per shard** — 50M queries is ~1–2 GB; bigger dictionaries are the pressure point. Levers: radix/prefix compression, a tighter dictionary, or a Bloom-filter-assisted fallback to the backend for the coldest prefixes.
3. **Prefix hot spots** — a popular prefix shards to one node by construction (`hash(prefix[0..2])`), which is *good* for locality but means a hot prefix saturates a node. Fix: duplicate the hot trie shards, or let the edge cache absorb it.
4. **Dictionary propagation** — pushing a new snapshot to 2,000 nodes must be fast; use a registry plus a push, with a pull fallback, and never require a coordinated restart.
5. **Long-tail backend QPS** — if the trie coverage is 95%, that 5% is 700 QPS, fine; if it degrades to 60%, it is 5.6k QPS on Solr, which will hurt.
6. **Trending pipeline lag** — 1-minute buckets must actually be 1 minute; lag makes trending useless.
7. **Cache pollution by bots**, which silently degrades the hit rate.
8. **Response serialization CPU** — at 14k RPS with JSON on 10 suggestions each, serialization is a real CPU cost; pre-serialize hot responses at the edge.
9. **Mobile-specific**: keyboard latency dominates perceived speed more than server latency on older devices; response size matters more than p99.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Suggest service down | **silent fallback to plain search** | client-side hide of the dropdown |
| One suggest node down | edge cache and other nodes absorb; ~1/N of misses fail | autoscaling, N+2 |
| Trie snapshot corrupted | node keeps its previous in-memory trie; load last-known-good | atomic swap + checksum verification |
| Redis (trending) down | trending boost = 0, popularity-only ranking | acceptable degradation |
| Long-tail backend slow | return trie-only results after a 15 ms timeout | partial results render instantly |
| Edge cache misses spike | origin QPS multiplies → potential origin outage | pre-warm popular prefixes; rate limit bots |
| Bot pollution | cache hit rate drops and responses degrade | per-device limits, anomaly-scored caching |
| Bad dictionary update | suggestion quality drops | version pinning + rollback to the previous snapshot |
| Serialization regression | p99 rises uniformly | pre-serialization at the edge; profiling |
| Locale data missing | fall back to global dictionary | per-locale snapshot completeness check |
| Unsafe content discovered post-hoc | prefix blocked retroactively via the blocked-prefix trie | purge affected cache entries |

Invariant: **typing must never be interrupted.** Autocomplete is an enhancement, and every failure path ends in a working search box.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Dictionary | trimmed in-memory trie | full corpus in the backend | 3–5B prefixes is not loadable; 50M queries carry the value |
| Storage | radix/FST trie + mmap snapshot | Postgres/MySQL prefix queries | in-memory is 100× faster; index seeks dominate the budget |
| Caching | PoP cache keyed without personalization | per-user caching | personalization in the key destroys the hit rate |
| Personalization | applied after cache on the top-k | personalized cache key | same reason; reorder is cheap |
| Trending | Redis 1-min buckets | batch-scored trend file | trends move in minutes, not days |
| Long tail | separate search backend | extend the trie to 500M | memory does not allow it |
| Response | pre-serialized/binary at the edge | JSON everywhere | serialization is a measurable share of the budget |
| Typo tolerance | targeted pre-filter | full fuzzy search | cost and false positives |
| Ranking | weighted log-linear with intent priors | ML ranker | 50 ms budget; a small GBDT is possible later, a neural ranker is not |
| Ordering | suggestions, not completions-first | full query completion | suggestions are more useful and cheaper to rank |

Rejected wholesale
- **A database `WHERE query LIKE 'gol%'` on every keystroke.** Index scans, connection exhaustion, and no typo tolerance.
- **Naive per-character prefix expansion** (26^k combinations). Explodes immediately.
- **Client-side full dictionary download.** Impossible for 500M queries; mobile data cost alone kills it.
- **One global dictionary node.** Single point of failure and a network hop for every keystroke.
- **Ranking at the edge.** Cannot access click logs or trends; keep ranking in the service, caching the raw candidate list if needed.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "How do you make this fast at 14,000 requests per second?"**
Model answer: Three layers of elimination. First, most keystrokes never leave the device — I debounce 30–50 ms, abort superseded requests, and keep an LRU of recent prefix→response pairs, so a user retyping "google" gets four of the six responses locally with no network at all. Second, the edge: PoP memory cache plus CDN, with a 70–85% hit rate because people type the same popular prefixes constantly and responses are near-identical across users, so the cache key deliberately excludes personalization. A hit is 5–10 ms. Third, the service tier: 2,000 stateless nodes each holding a ~1 GB radix trie over the top 50M queries, sharded on the first three prefix characters so a lookup touches exactly one shard and is O(prefix length) with no index seeks. Early termination stops traversal once I have limit×3 candidates, and hot responses are pre-serialized at the edge. The long-tail backend is consulted for maybe 5% of prefixes with a hard 15 ms timeout, and if it misses I return the trie's partial list, because a partial list renders instantly and the user is already typing the next character.

**Q2. "What about someone typing 'g' or 'go'?"**
Model answer: Short prefixes are the most requested and the least discriminating, so I precompute their answers. Anything under three characters gets a static, fully cacheable map of top suggestions per locale, built offline — that covers an enormous share of keystrokes and it is the cheapest possible path, essentially a CDN hit. Personalization is the only thing that changes at short prefixes, and it is applied by reordering the top few after the cache read rather than by varying the cache key, because a per-user cache key at that cardinality destroys the hit rate. I also reject one-character prefixes from most clients entirely as a product decision; there is almost no signal in "g" and it is where bot cache pollution concentrates. For two characters I allow it, but with the precomputed map and a short TTL so a new trending term can enter within a minute or two.

**Q3. "How do you handle spelling mistakes?"**
Model answer: In tiers, because full fuzzy search over 500M queries is unaffordable in a 50 ms budget. Tier one is a curated pre-filter: a small in-memory list of high-traffic queries and common typo patterns — "gogle", "amzon", "whtsup", "facebok" — matched by edit distance, which catches the overwhelming majority of real typos because real typos concentrate in the most-searched terms. Tier two is for the long tail: prefix-match first in the backend, and only apply a fuzzy expansion when the prefix returns fewer than the limit, keeping edit distance at 1 for short prefixes and 2 for prefixes of five characters or more, since distance 2 on a three-character prefix matches almost everything. Tier three is phonetic matching for the words where it helps most, like name spellings. I would not build a general n-gram fuzzy index across the whole corpus — the memory and CPU are not worth it, and the false-positive rate on two-letter suggestions is visibly bad. The ranking consequence also matters: if the user's input is a near-miss, I boost exact and near-miss completions so the correction is visible, and I do not persist the typo as a separate trending query, or trending fills with garbage.

**Q4. "How do you make trending work without breaking the cache?"**
Model answer: Trending is by nature fast-moving and by nature a cache killer, so the split is that trending lives *behind* the cache. The edge cache stores the prefix's candidate list with a 60–300 second TTL; the trending boost is applied in the service after the cache read, from a Redis sorted set per prefix that a Kafka consumer rebuilds every minute from the click stream. So a 5-minute-old cached candidate list gets re-ranked with current trend scores on every request, and the expensive part — the candidate generation — is still cached. Trending scores themselves decay with a roughly six-hour half-life so a spike fades rather than sticking, and the click stream that feeds them is sampled and batched rather than written synchronously from the request path. The abuse case I care about is coordinated manipulation: I cap the velocity any single query can gain, require some source diversity before a term can appear in the top-trending list, and route new top-trending entries through review. Otherwise the trending feature is a trivially gameable lever and it will be gamed within a week.

**Q5. "What does the click data buy you, and how do you use it?"**
Model answer: It is the only real quality signal available, and it closes the loop. Every click logs the prefix, the suggestion, its position, and a session hash to Kafka; a columnar sink aggregates it; a job recomputes exponentially decayed popularity per query and per suggestion position, which is what drives the trie scores and the ranking weights. Three concrete uses. First, dictionary curation: a query with high search volume but low CTR is a ranking failure, while a suggestion with high CTR at position zero is a ranking success, and those two facts let me tune weights rather than guess. Second, promotion and retirement: queries whose decayed popularity crosses a threshold get added to the trie snapshot, and queries that fall below it get retired, so the 50M-query budget is spent on what actually gets typed. Third, typo inference: high-volume prefixes with low CTR and a nearby high-CTR query are candidate misspellings, which feeds the curated typo list. The volume is why this is not synchronous — at 3.5k requests/second, writing per-keystroke logs directly would be the most expensive part of the system, so I batch client-side, sample at roughly 10%, and always log position-zero clicks unsampled because those matter most.

**Q6. "How would you scale this to a billion queries in the dictionary?"**
Model answer: I would not — the honest answer is that a larger dictionary makes the service worse. The marginal value of suggestions 51M through 1B is tiny, because those queries are almost always long enough that the user has typed enough to identify them, and a suggestion for them adds little. So the scaling work is about coverage efficiency rather than volume: radix/prefix compression to fit more per node, dropping to a Bloom-filter-assisted trie where the filter rules out whole subtrees so the cold tail never enters memory, and routing the remaining misses to the search backend. What genuinely does not scale is nodes — at that QPS you are into tens of thousands of pods, which makes dictionary propagation a real distributed-systems problem: a versioned snapshot artifact in object storage, a registry, atomic mmap swap per node, a pull fallback, and never a coordinated restart. The one thing I would add is sharding by locale rather than by prefix hash once the volume is large, since most traffic is regional and this eliminates cross-region latency entirely for most users. And I would keep the edge cache as the primary defense, because at that point no amount of trie engineering substitutes for not making the request.

---

## 12.5.16 Web crawler

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design a web crawler. Crawl the web continuously, respect robots.txt, handle 100 billion pages, dedupe content, and find new pages fast."

The score is in: **frontier management and politeness**, **URL canonicalization and dedupe**, **the crawl frontier as a priority queue**, **freshness vs depth**, **DNS and robots.txt as first-class components**, and **circuit breakers for crawl traps**.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Seed URLs, breadth/depth limits, per-domain politeness.
- F2. robots.txt fetch, cache, and enforcement including crawl-delay and wildcards.
- F3. Content extraction: HTML parse, canonical URL detection, text, links.
- F4. Dedupe by content hash and near-duplicate detection.
- F5. Sitemap.xml and RSS ingestion as a priority source.
- F6. Recrawl policy by content type and observed change rate.
- F7. Link graph for authority estimation (PageRank-like).
- F8. Per-user custom crawl scopes (a vertical search engine).

Non-functional
- N1. Crawl **1 billion URLs/day = ~11,500 QPS**, with a large burst factor.
- N2. Politeness: never exceed per-domain rates; robots.txt compliance is a hard requirement, not a nicety.
- N3. Freshness: news pages recrawled within minutes; evergreen content monthly.
- N4. Crawler resilience: a single pathological site must not consume capacity.
- N5. Storage: 100B pages indexed → a petabyte-scale store plus a link graph of ~10¹² edges.
- N6. Safety: no SSRF into private networks, no infinite crawl traps, bounded per-domain page counts.

---

**3. Estimation**

Volume
- 100B pages indexed, average 20 KB → **2 PB** of raw content. Compressed ~500 KB/page? No: 2 PB raw, ~1 PB compressed.
- Crawl rate 1B/day = 11.5k QPS, with per-worker efficiency: fetching a 20 KB page takes ~200 ms, so **one worker ≈ 5 pages/sec** → 11.5k QPS needs **~2,300 concurrent fetchers**. This is the number that sizes the fetcher pool.
- Bandwidth: 11.5k × 20 KB = **230 MB/s egress-ish** (ingress to us). Modest.

Storage breakdown
| Data | Size |
|---|---|
| raw HTML (compressed) | ~1 PB |
| extracted text | ~400 GB |
| link graph, ~100 avg outlinks/page | 100B × 100 = **10¹³ edges × 8 B = 80 TB** |
| URL/frontier metadata | 100B × 200 B = **20 TB** |
| dedupe index (hash → doc id) | 100B × 16 B = **1.6 TB** |

Arithmetic to state
- "The link graph at 10¹³ edges is bigger than the content. That is why crawlers store links compactly (adjacency lists, not SQL join tables) and why authority is a batch job, not a per-page computation."

---

**4. API design**

Internal (the crawler is a batch system; these are control-plane endpoints)
```
POST /crawl/jobs        { "seeds": [...], "scope": { "include": [...], "exclude": [...],
                          "maxDepth": 3, "maxPagesPerDomain": 500, "budget": "permalinks" },
                          "refreshPolicy": "weekly", "priority": "normal" }
-> 201 { "jobId": "j_9f3c", "state": "queued" }

GET  /crawl/jobs/{jobId}      -> { state, pagesCrawled, urlsDiscovered, robotsBlocked, errors }
GET  /crawl/jobs/{jobId}/events?cursor=  -> crawl events (SSE)
POST /crawl/jobs/{jobId}/cancel

GET  /frontier?domain=example.com&limit=200   -> queued URLs with scores
POST /frontier/reprioritize  { "domain":"x", "multiplier": 0.2 }   -> throttle
```

Indexer-facing
```
POST /index/documents   { "url":"https://...", "canonicalUrl":"...", "contentHash":"...",
                          "fetchedAt":"...", "httpStatus":200, "headers":{...},
                          "text":"...", "links":[{"href":"...","rel":"follow|nofollow"}],
                          "depth":2 }
-> 201 { "docId":"d_88f3", "deduped": false }
409 { "error":"duplicate_content", "canonicalDocId":"d_1234" }
```

Search-facing (the product output)
```
GET /search/index?urlPattern=...      -> index coverage and freshness per URL pattern
GET /robots?host=example.com          -> current cached robots.txt decision
```

Errors and required behaviors
| Case | Behavior |
|---|---|
| 404/410 | mark URL dead, do not recrawl until the refresh policy says otherwise |
| 429 or `Retry-After` | honor it; exponential backoff per host; enter a penalty box |
| 5xx burst from one host | circuit-break that host after N consecutive errors |
| robots.txt disallow | drop the URL, record why |
| robots.txt unreachable (5xx) | **treat as disallow-all** for that host (RFC 9309), retry later |
| DNS failure | host penalty, do not retry immediately |
| Redirect loop / > 10 hops | abandon; count as an error |
| Private/loopback IP (SSRF) | **refuse**; DNS-rebinding protection required |
| Content larger than cap (10 MB) | truncate and record; do not fetch unbounded |
| Crawl trap (infinite param space) | per-path-parameter canonicalization plus a per-directory URL budget |

---

**5. Data model**

```
frontier                        -- priority queue, sharded by host
  url           TEXT
  host          TEXT            -- shard key
  score         DOUBLE PRECISION  -- priority: authority + freshness + depth + type
  depth         INT
  parent_url    TEXT
  discovered_at TIMESTAMPTZ
  next_fetch_at TIMESTAMPTZ      -- politeness and backoff
  state         ENUM(queued, fetched, failed, dead)
  PRIMARY KEY (host, url)
```
```
robots_cache
  host, robots_txt, fetched_at, expires_at, sitemaps[], checksum
```
```
host_state                       -- one row per host; the politeness brain
  host, last_fetch_at, crawl_delay_ms, consecutive_errors, penalty_until,
  pages_fetched_today, total_pages, avg_response_ms, is_blocked
```
```
documents
  doc_id, url, canonical_url, content_hash, simhash, fetched_at,
  http_status, content_type, byte_size, depth, site_id, title
  INDEX (content_hash)
  INDEX (url)
```
```
link_graph                       -- compact adjacency, columnar/LSM, not a row-per-edge SQL table
  from_doc_id BIGINT, to_url_id BIGINT, anchor_text, rel, first_seen
  clustered by from_doc_id; compressed; batch-built into an ANN/authority index
```
```
crawl_errors / fetch_stats       -- for backoff and for search-result quality signals
```
```
crawl_budget
  job_id, pages_fetched, bytes_fetched, max_pages_per_domain, deadline
```

Key modeling choices: the frontier is **sharded by host** so politeness decisions and per-host state are local; `canonical_url` and `content_hash` are separate because the same content at 50 URLs is a duplicate while the same URL changing is an update; and near-duplicates are caught with **SimHash** over shingles (64-bit, Hamming distance ≤ 3) rather than exact hashes, which is the standard technique.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Scheduler / Prioritizer (batch + streaming)
   scores frontier entries: authority, freshness expectation, depth, content type, job priority
   emits to Fetch Scheduler
   |
   v
Fetch Scheduler (sharded by host)
   1. robots cache lookup  (miss -> Robots Fetcher, respects its own cache)
   2. host_state check: crawl-delay, penalty_until, daily page budget  -> defer if not allowed
   3. DNS resolution + SSRF guard (no private/loopback/link-local; re-check after resolve)
   4. HTTP GET with conditional headers: If-Modified-Since / If-None-Match
   5. circuit breaker per host
   |
   +--> Fetch Pool (2,300+ workers, async, HTTP/2, connection reuse, gzip/brotli)
   |
   v
Parser / Extractor (HTML parse, canonical link, link classification, robots meta, hreflang)
   |
   +--> Content Store (raw HTML, text)   [immutable, content-addressed]
   +--> Indexer  (dedupe by hash + SimHash, update inverted index, build link graph edges)
   +--> Link Extractor -> Frontier (new URLs, scored, deduplicated by a fast probabilistic filter)
   +--> Change Detector (conditional-request 304 -> update last_fetched only)
   +--> Authority Job (batch PageRank over the link graph)
```

The host sharding is the structural insight: politeness, rate limiting, and circuit breaking are all per-host decisions, so sharding by host makes them local state rather than distributed coordination.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Politeness and crawl traps: robots.txt, crawl-delay, and the traps that eat your budget**

- **robots.txt** is fetched per host, cached for its `Cache-Control`/heuristic lifetime (typically hours to a day), and evaluated for the specific user-agent. Wildcards and `Allow`/`Disallow` precedence with longest-match-wins must be implemented correctly — most implementations get `Allow: /x` vs `Disallow: /x/y` wrong. If robots.txt is **unreachable (5xx), treat it as disallow-all**, which is what RFC 9309 says and what avoids getting banned.
- **Crawl-delay** must be honored and additionally clamped by your own minimum. Use **per-host token buckets** for this, not per-crawler-worker: 100 workers must collectively fetch at the permitted rate, which is only enforceable with shared per-host state.
- **Conditional GET** is the polite and cheap thing: `If-None-Match`/`If-Modified-Since` and a correct 304 handling path. A 304 costs ~200 B instead of 20 KB, so freshness gets 100× cheaper.
- **Crawl traps** are the real budget killers: infinite `?session=`, calendars, faceted navigation, `/item/12345` sequences. Defenses: canonicalize aggressively (sort query params, strip tracking params like `utm_*`, `fbclid`, `sessionid`); cap URL length and the number of distinct query parameters; enforce a **per-directory URL budget** so a single path cannot contribute more than N URLs; detect *infinite-space* traps by noticing a high ratio of new URLs to documents actually indexed; and prefer `rel=nofollow` and `robots` meta directives.
- **Content traps** (calendars, endless comment pages): stop at depth limits and at "same content hash as the last three pages."
- **Circuit breakers per host**: N consecutive errors or a 429 storm puts the host in a penalty box with exponential backoff, so one broken site cannot consume the fetch pool. Bots that ignore `Retry-After` are how crawlers get blocked at scale, so honor it strictly.

**7.2 URL canonicalization and dedupe**

The pipeline:
1. **Parse and resolve**: relative → absolute, `rel=canonical` if present and same-site (trusted), otherwise our own normalization.
2. **Normalize**: lowercase scheme/host, strip default port, remove fragments, collapse `//`, remove trailing slash consistently, **sort query parameters**, strip known tracking parameters, drop the `index.html`, percent-encode consistently, and **resolve the path** (`/a/../b` → `/b`).
3. **Filter**: only http/https, no private/loopback/link-local IPs (SSRF guard re-checked after DNS resolution to prevent rebinding), no `mailto:`, size caps, MIME allowlist.
4. **Frontier dedupe**: a **probabilistic filter** (Bloom filter or Count-Min sketch over canonical URLs, ~1% false-positive rate) avoids a database round trip per discovered link. At 100B URLs a Bloom filter is ~12 GB at 1e-7 FP — cheap enough to be worth it.
5. **Content dedupe**: exact `SHA-256` for identical bytes; **SimHash** (64-bit over shingles) with Hamming distance ≤ 3 for near-duplicates (syndicated articles, mirrors, tracking pages). The canonical document is the one with the most inbound links or the best authority score.
6. **Same URL, changed content**: not a duplicate — a new fetch of the same canonical URL updates the document in place and invalidates derived indexes.

**7.3 Frontier scheduling: what to crawl next**

A pure FIFO frontier wastes your budget on low-value pages. Score entries:
```
score = w1*authority(page)
      + w2*freshnessExpectation(contentType, observedChangeRate)
      + w3*(1/(1+depth))
      + w4*jobPriority
      + w5*novelty(newURLRatioForHost, unseenNeighborhoodBonus)
      - w6*hostPenalty
```
Practical observations that score well:
- **Freshness prediction beats fixed intervals.** If a URL's hash changes on 80% of fetches, recrawl it in minutes; if it changes once a year, recrawl monthly. Maintain this per-URL-template, not per-URL, so it generalizes.
- **Depth is a proxy for value**, and news beats depth-8 legal pages for most verticals.
- **Authority from the link graph** (PageRank, computed in a batch job every few days, plus a freshness-weighted variant) is the single best generic signal.
- **Sitemaps and RSS outrank discovered links** because they are the publisher telling you what changed.
- **Sample the long tail** or you will spend all your bandwidth on the same 1M popular pages; and reserve a fixed fraction of budget for crawl-budget URLs (permalinks, deep content) because that is where the unique value is.
- **Per-host starvation avoidance**: a fair scheduler, or the head with a million links monopolizes the budget forever.

**7.4 Resilience and safety**

- **Fetch pool isolation**: timeouts at 10 s connect / 20 s total, per-host concurrency caps, and a **global budget** so one pathological host cannot exceed its share. Backoff on 429/503 with jitter.
- **DNS as a dependency**: resolve once, cache with a TTL, and guard against DNS rebinding by re-validating the resolved IP at connect time.
- **SSRF**: refuse private, loopback, link-local, and cloud metadata ranges (169.254.169.254 is the one that matters); this is a crawler-specific security requirement and a good thing to volunteer.
- **Memory safety**: cap response body size while streaming, cap decompression ratio (zip-bomb defense), and cap the number of links extracted from one page.
- **Parser safety**: HTML parsers are a classic source of CVEs. Isolate the parser in a sandbox, keep it patched, and treat extracted text and URLs as untrusted output — a page is attacker-controlled input.
- **Distributed robustness**: no global lock on the frontier; work stealing with per-host sharding; checkpointed job state so a worker crash does not lose progress.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **Fetcher concurrency** — 2,300+ concurrent connections is the sizing constraint, and it is bounded by memory and by the politeness limits you impose.
2. **Politeness per host** — the hard cap. Some sites permit 1 request/10 s; at scale the frontier's per-host queues become the bottleneck, not CPU.
3. **Frontier volume** — a large crawl discovers far more URLs than it can fetch; the frontier grows without bound, so scoring and eviction (drop low-score URLs past a horizon) matter.
4. **Bandwidth and storage write throughput** — 230 MB/s sustained ingest into 1–2 PB of content; that is a lot of sequential writes, so the content store must be sharded and append-oriented rather than row-oriented.
5. **The link graph** — 10¹³ edges. Authority computation is a batch job over a graph that does not fit in memory; use an iterative approach with partition-by-source and on-disk shuffle.
6. **Index building** — inverted index construction at 100B documents requires the index to be sharded by document-set, built in parallel, and atomically swapped.
7. **Duplicate detection cost** — hashing and SimHash on every fetched page, plus the near-duplicate search in the SimHash index.
8. **Change detection accuracy** — if your refresh policy is wrong you either waste bandwidth or serve stale results; freshness expectation is the metric that governs this.
9. **Legal/abuse** — blocked by site owners, DMCA, robots violations; needs a per-host policy and a way to honor a crawl-block request quickly.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| One host misbehaving (5xx, slow) | circuit breaker + penalty box; other hosts unaffected | exponential backoff, retry after cooldown |
| robots.txt unreachable | **treat as disallow-all for that host** | re-fetch later per cache policy |
| DNS outage for a region | fetches deferred | retry; do not fall back to a cached DNS answer past TTL |
| Frontier store degraded | crawl rate throttled to what the scheduler can commit | reduce fetch pool; preserve politeness invariants |
| Content store write failures | pages dropped from the index but links already extracted | retry queue; the frontier is unaffected |
| Indexer down | pages fetched but not searchable | index is a downstream projection; backlog then catch up |
| Parser crash on a specific format | that content type skipped, logged | sandbox the parser, patch, quarantine the host |
| Memory pressure from a huge page | body truncated at the cap | bounded streaming reads |
| A crawl job budget exceeded | job paused, checkpointed | resume from checkpoint |
| Duplicate storm (a site mirrors itself) | wasted bandwidth | content dedupe catches it after the first copy; SimHash near-dup detection |
| Frontier polluted with junk URLs | low-value crawl budget | canonicalization, per-directory caps, scoring eviction |
| Authority job behind | ranking quality degrades | prior index stays live; batch catches up |

Invariant: **politeness is never traded for throughput.** Violating robots or hammering a host gets the crawler blocked permanently, which is a much worse outcome than a smaller index.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ordering | priority frontier, not FIFO | BFS/DFS only | most budget should go to high-value, fresh URLs |
| Sharding | by host | by URL hash | politeness, rate limits, and circuit breaking become local |
| Politeness state | Redis-backed per-host token bucket | per-worker sleep | 100 workers must share one rate limit |
| Dedupe | Bloom filter at the frontier, then content hash + SimHash | DB uniqueness check per link | a round trip per discovered link is unaffordable |
| Canonicalization | aggressive (sorted params, tracking-param stripping) | minimal | the single biggest lever against duplicate and trap URLs |
| Freshness | predicted per URL template | fixed intervals | bandwidth and result quality both improve |
| Indexing | async, sharded, atomic swap | synchronous per page | decouples crawl throughput from index build |
| Authority | batch PageRank over a partitioned graph | on-the-fly link analysis | 10¹³ edges cannot be analyzed per request |
| Robots on 5xx | disallow-all | allow-all | RFC 9309; and getting banned is catastrophic |
| Raw content store | immutable, content-addressed | mutable rows | re-crawl becomes a new object; old versions are cheap to keep |
| Fetch layer | async HTTP with per-host concurrency caps | one thread per host | predictable memory and precise rate control |

Rejected wholesale
- **"Just crawl the whole web" with no frontier scoring.** Budget goes almost entirely to the same popular pages.
- **Bypassing robots.txt for a vertical.** Legally indefensible and reputationally fatal.
- **Storing HTML in a relational database.** 1 PB of blobs in rows is a restore disaster.
- **One global frontier queue.** Contention and no per-host isolation.
- **Computing PageRank per document at fetch time.** Batch or nothing.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "How do you decide what to crawl next?"**
Model answer: A priority queue scored per URL. Authority from the link graph is the biggest generic term — PageRank, batch-computed and freshness-weighted, so a page that keeps earning links outranks a stale one. Second is freshness expectation: I record per URL-template how often the content hash actually changes when fetched, and a URL whose hash changes 80% of the time gets recrawled in minutes while a URL that changes yearly gets monthly. That single signal improves both result quality and bandwidth, and it generalizes far better than per-URL intervals. Third is depth, since shallow pages are usually more valuable and always cheaper. Fourth is publisher-sourced priority: sitemaps and RSS beat discovered links because the publisher is telling you what changed. Fifth is a novelty term, so a host with new URLs gets explored and a host that only links to itself does not. And everything is multiplied by host penalty and budget. Two things I would insist on: a fixed fraction of the budget reserved for crawl-budget URLs, because that is where the unique long-tail value is and BFS will never get there, and explicit eviction of low-score URLs past a horizon, because the frontier otherwise grows forever and mostly with junk.

**Q2. "Someone argues you don't need robots.txt. What's your answer?"**
Model answer: Three reasons, and the first is decisive. Practically, violating robots.txt is how crawlers get blocked — by IP, by User-Agent, permanently — and a blocked crawler loses a domain entirely, which is a much bigger loss than any pages it would have gained. Legally, robots.txt is a recognized access-control signal and ignoring it in the US after a `noindex`-style ruling has been treated as a violation of the Terms of Service, and there is active litigation around it; the safe posture is compliance. And technically, most of the web's crawlable value is not on the pages robots.txt disallows — it is on the content those pages link to. I also implement it correctly rather than approximately: `Allow`/`Disallow` with longest-match-wins, wildcards, per-User-Agent groups, and the meta robots tag on the page. And if robots.txt returns a 5xx, I treat it as disallow-all, which is what RFC 9309 says and what keeps you out of the penalty box. Being strict costs a little coverage and avoids a class of failure you cannot recover from.

**Q3. "A site is slow to respond. What do you do without hurting yourself?"**
Model answer: Per-host state with three layers of protection. First, a concurrency cap of one or two in-flight requests per host, so slow responses cannot tie up a large share of the fetch pool — this is why the frontier is sharded by host rather than by URL, because it makes these decisions local. Second, timeouts at 10 seconds connect and 20 seconds total, after which the request is abandoned and the slot released. Third, a per-host penalty: consecutive timeouts or 5xx put the host into a penalty box with exponential backoff, and a `429` or `Retry-After` is honored exactly as specified, because ignoring Retry-After is precisely how a crawler gets banned at scale. Once a host is in the penalty box, the frontier entries for it stay queued with a `next_fetch_at` in the future, so nothing is lost — the crawl is just delayed for that host. If a whole region of sites is slow, the global circuit breaker reduces the fetch pool rather than violating politeness, because I would rather crawl fewer pages politely than crawl fast and get blocked everywhere.

**Q4. "How do you avoid crawling the same thing twice, and how big does that problem get?"**
Model answer: Two different problems, so two mechanisms. The first is URL-level: discovered links are pushed through a fast probabilistic filter — a Bloom filter over canonicalized URLs at around a 1e-7 false-positive rate is about 12 GB for 100 billion URLs, which is trivially affordable and avoids a database round trip for every link on every page. Canonicalization has to come first, or the Bloom filter fills with near-duplicates: lowercase scheme and host, strip the fragment, collapse `//`, resolve `..`, sort query parameters, drop tracking parameters like `utm_*` and `fbclid`, and remove `index.html`. Then the second problem is content-level, because a different URL can serve identical bytes: exact `SHA-256` for byte-identical pages, and SimHash over shingles with a Hamming distance of 3 or less for near-duplicates like syndicated articles and mirrors, which exact hashes will never catch. The canonical document is chosen as the copy with the most inbound links or the highest authority, and the duplicates are recorded as aliases so the index does not double-count them. Worth saying explicitly: a URL whose content changes is *not* a duplicate — it is an update, which is why canonical URL and content hash are separate fields.

**Q5. "How do you deal with a crawl trap that generates infinite URLs?"**
Model answer: Traps are the main way a crawler burns its entire budget, so they get several defenses. Canonicalization handles the easy cases — `?session=`, `?utm_source=`, `?page=` loops, and infinite `..` paths. Then per-directory URL budgets: a cap on distinct URLs accepted under any single path prefix, which makes an infinite space finite. Then a global cap on the number of distinct query parameters and on URL length, and rejecting URLs with more than N parameters because real pages do not have those. Then novelty detection at the host level: if a host's ratio of newly discovered URLs to actually indexed documents is very high, it is generating URLs that resolve to nothing, and the crawler throttles or quarantines that host. And a content-level stop: if a URL's content hash matches any of the last few pages fetched from that host, it is almost certainly an endless list, so stop descending that branch. I would also enforce a hard job-level budget — pages, bytes, and a deadline — with checkpointing, so even a trap I failed to detect costs a bounded amount rather than the crawl.

**Q6. "How do you keep a 100-billion-page index fresh without re-crawling everything?"**
Model answer: Conditional requests plus predicted change rates. Every fetch sends `If-None-Match` and `If-Modified-Since`, so a 304 costs a few hundred bytes instead of 20 KB — for a page that changes once a year that is roughly a 100× bandwidth saving, and I treat 304 handling as a first-class code path because it is where a surprising fraction of crawlers get their efficiency wrong. On top of that I estimate per URL-template change probability from the observed history of hash changes, which lets me schedule: a template whose hash changes most fetches is recrawled in minutes, a template that never changes is recrawled quarterly or not at all. That single signal is what makes a billion-page refresh affordable. Priority then goes to the pages most likely to have changed and most likely to matter — high authority, news-like templates, sitemaps and RSS as publisher signals — with a reserved slice of budget for crawl-budget URLs so the long tail is not starved by the head. And I would also lean on the fact that the index is a projection: it is fully rebuildable from the content store and the link graph, so an index failure is a rebuild, not a data loss.

---

## 12.5.17 Google Docs collaborative editing

⚫ Expert · **Deep dive**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design real-time collaborative document editing. Multiple people typing at once, offline support, and it never loses anyone's work."

The score is in: knowing that this is **not** a CRDT-vs-OT quiz but a systems problem — operation log, ordering, presence, causal delivery, snapshotting, and the offline/merge path. Bonus points for knowing Google Docs' actual approach (operational transformation with a server as the ordering point, plus periodic snapshots).

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Real-time multi-user editing with concurrent cursor/selection visibility.
- F2. Insert, delete, format, and structural operations.
- F3. Offline editing with later reconciliation.
- F4. Version history, named versions, restore.
- F5. Comments, suggestions (track changes), permissions, sharing.
- F6. Presence: online users, cursor positions, "X is editing".
- F7. Multiple document types (doc, sheet, slide) and export.
- F8. Search, and "see all changes while I was away".

Non-functional
- N1. **Local echo latency < 50 ms** — the keypress must render instantly on the typing device, regardless of network.
- N2. Remote cursor propagation **< 150 ms p99**.
- N3. Availability 99.99%+ for the session gateway; editing must survive a server restart with no lost work.
- N4. Convergence: all replicas reach byte-identical documents.
- N5. No operation is ever lost — acknowledged operations are durable within ~1 s.
- N6. Documents up to millions of operations; the active working set in memory must be bounded.

---

**3. Estimation**

Scale
- 100M DAU, 5% editing at any moment → **5M concurrent editing sessions**; ~200M documents/day created.
- Operations: average typing ~4 ops/sec while actively typing → 5M sessions × maybe 15% actively typing × 4 = **3M ops/sec peak**.
- Average document accumulates a few thousand operations over its life; long-lived docs can reach millions.

Storage
- Operation log: ~40 B per op (a compact JSON/protobuf delta plus author and timestamp). 3M ops/sec × 40 B = **120 MB/s** sustained if all were persisted forever, which they are not — logs are compacted into snapshots.
- Snapshot per document every ~100 ops: 200M docs/day is far too much, so snapshot on a **time-and-size policy**: every 100 ops or every 60 s of inactivity, whichever comes first. Snapshot size ~ the document text: average 10 KB → 200M × 10 KB = **2 TB/day** of snapshots, retained 30 days with older versions in cold storage.
- So the write pattern is: **append ops to a log, periodically compact into a snapshot, expire the log.** That is the key storage design.

Bandwidth
- 5M sessions × ~1 KB/s of ops + presence = **~5–10 GB/s** across session gateways. Websocket fan-out, not HTTP polling.

Arithmetic to state
- "5 million concurrent sessions at 3 million ops/sec is a session-routing problem, not a data problem. Each op is ~40 bytes, so the storage side is cheap; the hard part is 5 million long-lived stateful connections and knowing where each one's log lives."

---

**4. API design**

```
WS /edit/v1/documents/{docId}?sessionId=...&ackToken=...
   -> { "sessionId":"s_1", "assignedEpoch":"e_7", "revision":18422,
        "snapshotId":"snap_9001", "pendingOps":[], "access":"edit" }

Client -> server
{ "type":"op", "clientId":"c_ab", "seq":41, "baseRevision":18422,
  "ops":[ {"retain":12,"insert":"hello"}, {"delete":3} ] }
{ "type":"ack", "revision":18423 }
{ "type":"presence", "cursor":{ "index":1204, "user":"u_9" } }
{ "type":"ping", "t":1767000000000 }

Server -> client
{ "type":"committed", "revision":18423, "serverTime":1767000000123,
  "transformed":[ ... ], "originClientId":"c_ab" }
{ "type":"remote", "revision":18424, "clientId":"c_cd",
  "ops":[ {"retain":1180,"insert":" world"} ] }
{ "type":"presence", "users":[ {"userId":"u_9","name":"Lena","color":"#f80","cursor":1204,"selection":[1204,1234]} ] }
{ "type":"error", "code":"revision_conflict", "action":"resync_required" }
{ "type":"versionSnapshot", "versionId":"v_88", "createdBy":"u_9" }
```
Offline
```
POST /edit/v1/documents/{docId}/ops:batch   { "ops":[...], "fromRevision": 18400, "baseSnapshotId":"snap_8" }
-> 200 { "results": [ {"revision":18450, "transformed":[...]}, ... ] }
```
Error semantics
| Case | Behavior |
|---|---|
| Revision too old to transform | 409 + fresh snapshot; client rebases its pending ops against it (**never lose local work**) |
| Access revoked mid-session | close with 4403; pending local ops are preserved and exported |
| Socket dropped | reconnect with the last acked revision; ops are replayed idempotently by `clientId`+`seq` |
| Snapshot missing | rebuild the document by replaying the log from the last known good snapshot |
| Doc size limit | reject new inserts beyond the cap with a clear message |
| Large paste | client splits into chunked ops with a size cap |

`clientId` + `seq` idempotency and the `baseRevision` on every op are the two fields that make reconnect and replay correct.

---

**5. Data model**

```
documents
  doc_id, owner_id, title, created_at, revision BIGINT, snapshot_id,
  acl JSONB, type ENUM(doc, sheet, slide), size_bytes, created_by
```
```
operation_log                  -- append-only, sharded by doc_id
  doc_id, revision BIGINT, client_id, seq, base_revision,
  ops BYTEA,      -- compact retain/insert/delete encoding, NOT full text
  actor_id, server_ts, created_at
  PRIMARY KEY (doc_id, revision)
  -- retained until compacted into a snapshot
```
```
snapshots
  snapshot_id, doc_id, revision, content BYTEA (or object-store ref),
  content_hash, created_at, created_by, kind ENUM(auto, named, restore)
```
```
document_versions   -- user-visible history; named + auto checkpoints
  doc_id, version_id, snapshot_id, created_at, created_by, label
```
```
presence            -- ephemeral, Redis, TTL 30-60s
  doc:{docId}:presence -> { clientId: {userId, name, color, cursor, selection, ts} }
```
```
session_directory
  doc_id -> { clientId -> gatewayNodeId }   TTL 2 min
```
```
acl / permissions     -- Postgres, replicated; checked at session start and re-checked periodically
```

The critical modeling point: **the operation log stores *deltas*, not document text.** Storing a full document per operation is O(n²) over a document's life and is what naive implementations do wrong. Retain/insert/delete encoding keeps an op at ~20–40 bytes.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Editor Client (local doc model, offline queue, undo/redo, IndexedDB cache)
  |  WS (one per tab)
  v
Session Gateway Cluster (stateless, 100k sessions/node; holds sessions in memory,
                         writes ops to the log, fans out)
  |  ordered append per doc
  v
Operation Log (sharded by doc_id, PRIMARY KEY (doc_id, revision) -> revisions are total-ordered per doc)
  |  compaction
  v
Snapshot Service -> object storage / big value store

Per-doc sequence: the log partition for a document is a single-writer ordered log, so
revisions are globally ordered per document. This is what makes OT straightforward.

Offline path: client keeps pending ops + base snapshot -> POST /ops:batch -> server
transforms against current revision -> returns new revisions -> client re-applies.

Presence: client -> gateway -> Redis (ephemeral) -> broadcast to other sessions in the doc
```

Diagram: client ↔ gateway, gateway → per-doc ordered log → snapshots, plus the offline batch path and Redis presence.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Why OT (or CRDT) and what the trade-off really is**

- **OT (operational transformation)** — what Google Docs historically used. Each operation carries a base revision; the server transforms an incoming op against every op committed after its base revision (the `transform(op, against)` function), producing a new op valid at the head revision. All clients converge. Requires a central ordering point (the server) and a correct transformation function per operation type — notoriously subtle and bug-prone, but each client only needs the operations it has not seen.
- **CRDTs (Yjs/Automerge)** — operations are commutative, so there is no central ordering requirement and offline merging works without a server transform step. The cost is metadata: a CRDT document's memory grows with the number of *concurrent* edits and needs explicit garbage collection of tombstones, and the wire format is heavier. Yjs's binary encoding is genuinely small in practice, but pathological concurrent editing (many offline clients) inflates it.
- **Practical answer**: production systems converge on CRDTs for new products because offline merge is much easier to get right, and on OT-with-server-sequencing where the document model and operation set are small and controlled. What matters for the interview is that you can state the trade-off crisply and then talk about the *systems* problem, which is the real content.
- **The systems problem that OT does not solve for you**: you still need an ordered per-document log, you still need acknowledgements and durability, you still need presence, and you still need the client to never lose local work. That is where most candidates run out of material, so spend your time there.

**7.2 Durability, acknowledgement, and "no work is ever lost"**

- The client renders the keystroke **immediately from its local model** and queues the op locally (IndexedDB) before sending. Local echo is under 50 ms and does not depend on the network at all.
- The server appends to the per-document log, and only **after the append is durable** does it send the `committed` ack. An op that is not acked stays in the client's queue.
- Reconnect replays queued ops with the same `clientId` + `seq`, and the server dedupes on that pair, so replay is idempotent.
- **Snapshotting and compaction**: every ~100 ops or 60 s of inactivity, the server writes a snapshot and can truncate the log before that revision. Snapshot writes must be cheap — write content to object storage and keep only the pointer in the log.
- **Version history** is a separate concern from compaction: user-visible named and auto checkpoints must be retained far longer than the compaction window, and a restore creates a *new* revision rather than rewinding the log. Rewinding is how you lose work.
- **Never delete an unacknowledged op.** If a client is offline for a week it accumulates a large op queue; the batch endpoint transforms it against the current revision and returns per-op results so the client can see which ops failed rather than discovering it on conflict.

**7.3 Session routing and the 5-million-session problem**

- Every session must know which gateway holds its peers. `session_directory` in Redis maps `doc:{docId}` to `{clientId → gatewayId}`. On connect, a gateway registers itself and reads the directory; on a fan-out, it publishes to the listed gateways, which deliver locally.
- **Sessions are stateful but not durable**, which is the point: gateway loss means sockets drop, clients reconnect, and the log plus snapshot is the durable state.
- **Document affinity**: to avoid every op crossing the country, hash `doc_id` so that all sessions for a document prefer the same region, and pin sessions for a document to the same gateway pool. Redis is a shared dependency but not on the per-op path — gateways hold a local copy of the directory and refresh it periodically, so a Redis blip degrades fan-out quality rather than stopping editing.
- **Backpressure**: a client on a slow link must not stall the gateway. Cap outbound queue size per connection; when exceeded, drop presence updates first (they are advisory), then apply **backpressure to the *source*** of remote ops by asking the client to re-fetch from a snapshot. Never drop committed document operations.
- **Presence is best-effort and TTL'd**: a 30–60 s TTL means a crashed client disappears without cleanup logic.

**7.4 Editing correctness: formatting, undo, and the everyday bugs**

- **Formatting operations are not like text operations.** A user bolding a range that another user is concurrently deleting produces a document with formatting on nothing. OT for attributes needs per-character attribute sets with transformation rules; CRDTs represent formatting as per-character LWW registers, which converges more simply. Either way, "apply formatting to an empty selection" must be a defined no-op or a defined insertion-format, not undefined.
- **Undo must be per-user and OT-aware.** Undoing your own insert after someone else has inserted inside it cannot simply delete your text; it must transform. This is the classic OT subtlety and a good thing to volunteer.
- **Client-side must not trust ordering.** The client applies remote ops optimistically for latency and reconciles on `committed`; a mismatch triggers a resync from the server snapshot rather than a guess.
- **Delete-vs-typing** produces "someone deleted what I was typing" — the canonical demo of the problem. Concurrency control, not UI, fixes it.
- **Test strategy**: property-based and fuzz testing on the transform function, plus a multi-client simulator that fires randomized operations against a reference implementation. Convergence bugs are the class of bug that shows up as user reports in the field, so this testing is not optional.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **5M concurrent sessions** — the sizing constraint. Sessions are long-lived, so deploys require draining millions of sockets gradually; clients reconnect and replay, which must be cheap and idempotent.
2. **Fan-out latency** — one hot document with 200 people in it is a hotspot in the Redis directory, the gateway fan-out, and the log partition. Real mitigations: presence is polled/coalesced rather than pushed per keystroke, and a hard cap or read-only mode for pathological documents.
3. **Per-document ordering** is inherently serial. One document = one ordered log partition, so a single document's throughput ceiling is the log partition's write rate. Very high-activity documents are the hard case; mitigation is batching and rejecting concurrent-edit limits for them.
4. **Memory in the gateway** — each session holds the document working set. Large documents (millions of ops) must be windowed: keep the recent working set in memory and read older content from the snapshot.
