5. **Snapshot cost** — snapshotting a document being edited rapidly competes with op writes. Snapshot in the background and asynchronously; never block op acknowledgement on a snapshot.
6. **Redis for the session directory** — a dependency on the fan-out path. Local caches plus periodic refresh keep it off the critical path.
7. **Storage growth** — snapshots at 2 TB/day with 30-day retention is 60 TB; compaction policy and cold storage for old versions are required.
8. **Reconnect storms** after a deploy or outage. Exponential backoff with jitter, and a load-shedding mode that accepts connections but degrades presence.
9. **Offline merge cost** — a week-long offline client can submit thousands of ops; the batch transform is O(pending × concurrent), so it needs a cap and a clear rejection path.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Invariant |
|---|---|---|
| Gateway node crash | its sessions drop; clients reconnect with the last acked revision and replay | no acknowledged op is lost; replay is idempotent |
| Log partition unavailable | edits for that document pause; clients queue locally | the client never blocks on the network |
| Redis (session directory) down | fan-out quality degrades; editing continues via local cache + refresh | editing is not blocked by Redis |
| Snapshot service down | log grows; compaction deferred | ops are still appended and acked |
| Snapshot corrupt/missing | rebuild by replaying the log from the last good snapshot | index is derived and rebuildable |
| Client too old to transform | 409 + fresh snapshot; client rebases its pending ops | local work is preserved and offered to the user |
| Permission revoked mid-session | session closed with 4403; pending ops exported | the user can always retrieve their own unacked work |
| Slow client | outbound queue capped; presence dropped first; document ops never dropped | committed ops are never lost |
| Log revision skew | server detects a gap and returns 409 | a client never silently applies ops out of order |
| Large document / windowed memory | older content read from snapshot on demand | editing remains responsive |
| Reconnect storm | connection shedding; presence reduced | the system stays up under a thundering herd |
| Transform-function bug | divergence detected by the checksum/snapshot comparison; resync forced | convergence is verified, not assumed |
| Network partition between two clients | both edit offline; both merge on reconnect via the batch endpoint | convergence with no data loss |

Invariant: **an acknowledged operation is durable, and an unacknowledged operation is never discarded.**

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Concurrency | OT with server sequencing, or a CRDT library | naive last-write-wins | LWW destroys concurrent edits |
| Central ordering | per-document ordered log | unordered gossip | a total order per document makes transformation tractable |
| Log content | deltas (retain/insert/delete) | full document per op | O(n²) over a document's life |
| Durability | append-then-ack | ack-then-append | an acked op must be durable |
| Snapshots | time+size policy, background | every N ops synchronously | snapshot must not block ops |
| Versions | new revision from a restore | rewind the log | rewinding loses work |
| Session state | in-memory + Redis directory | database-backed sessions | sessions are ephemeral by nature |
| Presence | TTL'd, best-effort, coalesced | strongly consistent | nobody needs a linearizable cursor |
| Undo | per-user, transform-aware | single global undo stack | global undo undoes other people's edits |
| Documents | single-region affinity | fully distributed session | fan-out is the hot path |
| Backpressure | cap outbound, drop presence first | unbounded queue or drop document ops | document ops are the data |
| Offline | local queue + batch transform endpoint | block editing offline | offline support is a requirement |

Rejected wholesale
- **Last-write-wins on document snapshots.** Deletes everything concurrently typed.
- **Storing the whole document per version.** Storage explodes and merge is impossible.
- **HTTP polling for real-time updates.** Latency and load are both unacceptable.
- **A global undo stack.** Unsellable to users; it undoes their colleagues' work.
- **Synchronous snapshotting inside the op-commit path.** Snapshot latency becomes edit latency.
- **Treating presence as strongly consistent state.** It is advisory and must be allowed to be stale.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "OT or CRDT — which do you pick and why?"**
Model answer: For a new product I would use a CRDT, because the hard part of collaborative editing is not the algebra, it is offline merge. OT requires a correct transformation function per operation pair, which means months of subtle bug-finding, and it requires a central ordering point, which makes a week-long offline session genuinely hard — you are transforming thousands of pending ops against everything committed in the interim. A CRDT makes concurrent operations commutative, so the offline merge is "send my ops, apply them in arrival order" with no transform function to get wrong; the cost is metadata, since tombstones and version vectors inflate memory and need explicit garbage collection, and pathological concurrent editing makes the wire format heavier. Where OT still wins is a controlled document model and operation set — a spreadsheet with column-oriented semantics, for instance, where a general CRDT gives you the wrong merge behavior for formulas anyway. Either way, the systems work is the same: an ordered per-document log, ack only after durable append, periodic snapshots, and a client that never loses local work. I would spend the interview time there rather than on which algorithm won.

**Q2. "How do you guarantee no one loses work?"**
Model answer: Four mechanisms, and the most important one is on the client. The client applies the keystroke to its local model immediately and writes the operation to a local durable queue (IndexedDB) before sending it, so a crash, a killed tab, or a dead battery loses nothing — local echo is under 50 ms and depends on nothing. Second, the server appends to the per-document log and only acknowledges after that append is durable, so an unacknowledged op is the server's problem to resolve, not the user's. Third, reconnect replays the queue idempotently with `clientId` plus `seq`, and the server dedupes on that pair, so a duplicate replay is a no-op. Fourth, if the client's base revision is too old to transform against — a week offline, or the op set is too large — the server returns 409 with a fresh snapshot and the client rebases its pending ops onto it, preserving them; if rebasing is not possible, the client exports the local work so the user can copy it out rather than losing it. And a version restore creates a new revision rather than rewinding the log, which is what makes "undo" safe.

**Q3. "How do you handle 5 million concurrent sessions?"**
Model answer: Sessions are long-lived and stateful, so this is really a routing and deployment problem. Each tab opens one websocket to a session gateway; the gateway holds the session in memory, keeps an in-process copy of the document working set, and registers itself in a Redis session directory mapping document to client to gateway so fan-out can find peers. The per-document ordered log is sharded by `doc_id`, which means all sessions for a document naturally prefer one partition and I can pin them to the same region, so an op does not cross the country. Deploys are the operational hard part: with millions of long-lived sockets I cannot restart the fleet at once, so new fleets come up alongside, connections drain gradually over hours, and clients reconnect with their last acked revision and replay idempotently. Redis is deliberately off the per-op critical path — gateways refresh the directory periodically, so a Redis blip degrades fan-out quality rather than stopping editing. And backpressure is per connection: cap the outbound queue, drop presence updates first because they are advisory, and if a client still cannot keep up, apply backpressure to the source and let it resync from a snapshot. Committed document operations are never dropped, ever.

**Q4. "User A is typing while user B deletes that line. What happens?"**
Model answer: That is the canonical conflict and the answer depends on the algorithm, not on the UI. With OT, A's insert arrives at a base revision before B's delete, so when B's op comes in the server transforms it against A's insert, and B's delete covers only the characters that actually existed at the time B looked at the screen — the transform function's whole job is to keep the deletion from spilling into text that arrived after B's base revision. The result converges on every replica, and what the user sees is that B deleted the line as they saw it while A's concurrent characters survive. With a CRDT it falls out of commutativity rather than a transform function: the delete removes only the specific elements B targeted, and A's concurrent insert is a different element that survives. The UX then matters as much as the convergence: the surviving text should be attributed and, ideally, offer "B deleted the line you were typing on — restore." Two related cases worth raising: formatting applied to a selection that another user just deleted should be a defined no-op rather than an error, and **undo must be per-user and transform-aware** — undoing your own insert after someone else typed inside it cannot simply delete your text, it has to transform, which is the subtlest bug in every OT implementation.

**Q5. "How do you store the history?"**
Model answer: An append-only operation log plus periodic snapshots, and the encoding choice is what makes it affordable. The log stores *deltas* — retain/insert/delete against a position — at roughly 20–40 bytes per op, never the whole document, because storing full text per operation is quadratic over a document's life. The log is keyed `(doc_id, revision)` so revisions are totally ordered per document, which is exactly what transformation needs. Every ~100 ops or 60 seconds of inactivity, a background job writes a snapshot of the current content to object storage and records the pointer in the log, after which the log before that revision can be truncated. Snapshots are written off the acknowledgement path, so snapshot latency never becomes edit latency. User-visible history is separate: named and automatic checkpoints are retained far longer than the compaction window, and a restore creates a *new revision* from an old snapshot rather than rewinding the log — rewinding is how you destroy work that was committed after the restore point. Old versions tier to cold storage; the recent working set stays in gateway memory.

**Q6. "How do you test this?"**
Model answer: Property-based and randomized testing, because this is the one system where the bugs are emergent rather than per-function. The core is a transform/merge function tested against properties: inserting at the same position from two clients converges; deleting what another client just typed does not delete more than it saw; applying operations in any interleaving produces byte-identical results across replicas; undo after a concurrent edit restores only that user's text; and the sequence length is bounded. Then a multi-client simulator that fires randomized operations from N clients with randomized network delays, drops, and reorders, against a reference implementation, asserting convergence after every round — this is what finds the bugs you cannot enumerate. Third, chaos: kill gateways mid-typing and assert that on reconnect the client has no gaps and no duplicates; kill the snapshot service and assert ops still commit; partition two clients and assert the batch merge converges. Fourth, large-scale determinism checks: clients compare content hashes of the snapshot against the server's, and divergence triggers a forced resync rather than silent corruption. And finally, dogfooding with real-time presence of bugs in a "debug session" mode that logs every transform, which has historically found more bugs than any test.

---

## 12.5.18 Distributed cache

⚫ Expert · **Deep dive**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design a distributed cache in front of our database. Tens of millions of keys, sub-millisecond reads, and it must not become a source of truth."

The score is in: **consistency and invalidation** (the real problem), **eviction under pressure**, **hot key / skew handling**, **the stampede**, **memory efficiency**, and **being honest that "consistent distributed cache + database" is usually an anti-pattern**.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Get/set/setnx/delete/expire/increment with atomic operations.
- F2. TTL-based and LRU/LFU eviction.
- F3. Namespaces and per-tenant quotas.
- F4. Pub/sub for invalidation across processes.
- F5. Optional persistence/restartability.
- F6. Optional two-tier (memory + local L1).

Non-functional
- N1. **< 1 ms p99** from within a region; ~1 ms is also the round-trip floor for a network call, so a remote cache is barely better than the database.
- N2. Tens of millions to billions of keys. At ~500 B/key, 100M keys = **50 GB per replica**.
- N3. Availability: the cache is an *optimization*. Its outage must degrade to origin latency, not to an outage.
- N4. Memory-bounded and predictable under a key-cardinality explosion.
- N5. No single hot shard; no single point of failure.
- N6. Eviction must never be able to evict data that is the only copy of something.

---

**3. Estimation**

Working set
- 30M active users × ~10 hot keys/user = **300M keys**, average 300 B → **90 GB** of live data.
- At 3 replicas for durability/availability, **270 GB deployed**.
- Reasonable ratio: the cache should hold 10–20% of the origin's data; if you need more than that, you have a model problem, not a cache problem.

QPS
- A social app: 20k QPS reads against the cache. At ~100k ops/sec per shard (single-threaded Redis), **1–3 shards** is enough for throughput; 8–16 for headroom and rebalancing.
- Egress/cost: 20k QPS × 300 B = 6 MB/s — negligible. The cost is memory and operations, not bandwidth.

Latency arithmetic, which is the whole design constraint
- In-process L1: **~0.05 µs**.
- Same-host socket: ~50 µs.
- Same-AZ Redis: **~200–500 µs**.
- Cross-AZ Redis: ~1 ms.
- Cross-region Redis: **5–20 ms**, which is worse than the database it fronts.

So the design conclusion: **you cannot get sub-millisecond across regions.** Regional caches with local L1s and explicit invalidation are the only way; a single global cache is architecturally impossible at this latency budget. This is the calculation that justifies the topology.

Memory overhead
- Raw data plus key, hash-table slot, and object overhead: ~30–50% overhead for small values. Storing 90 GB of user data costs ~120 GB of process memory. This is why small-value caches (Redis) hit limits long before raw data suggests, and why compression or a compact encoding matters above ~500 B values.

---

**4. API design**

```
GET  /cache/{ns}/{key}                 -> 200 { "v": <value>, "ttlMs": 12340, "hitSource": "l2" }
                                         404 (and a negative-cache marker)
PUT  /cache/{ns}/{key}                 { "v": <value>, "ttlMs": 60000, "casToken": "..." }  -> 204
DELETE /cache/{ns}/{key}               -> 204  (async invalidation elsewhere)
POST /cache/{ns}:batch                 { "ops": [ {"op":"mget","keys":[...]},
                                                 {"op":"set","key":"k","v":1,"ttlMs":5000},
                                                 {"op":"incr","key":"c","by":1} ] }
                                         -> { "results":[ ... ], "partial": false }
POST /cache/{ns}/{key}:lock            { "ttlMs": 5000, "fencingToken": 8812 }
DELETE /cache/{ns}/{key}:lock          (with the fencing token)
POST /invalidations                    { "ns":"user:9f3c", "keys":[...], "origin":"node-17" }
                                         -> 202  (pub/sub to subscribers)
GET  /health, /stats, /evictions, /hitrate{by_ns}
```

Key semantic decisions to state explicitly
- **Cache-aside, not write-through**, for read-mostly data; write-through only where the origin write already goes through the cache anyway.
- **Negative caching** with a short TTL for misses — a 404 storm must not reach the origin.
- **CAS / fencing tokens** rather than naive `SETNX` locks, because a client that stalls past its lock TTL can otherwise release someone else's lock.
- **A `hitSource` field** so you can measure L1 vs L2 hit rates; without it you cannot tune the tiering.
- Errors: `429` for cardinality/quota abuse, `413` for oversized values, `507` for memory pressure, and always an explicit `partial: true` on batch results so a partial batch is never mistaken for a full one.

---

**5. Data model**

```
keyspace layout
  {ns}:{key}                          -- hash tags for cluster slot affinity
  ttl                                   -- absolute expiry (ms epoch)
  value                                 -- bytes
  lru_clock (Redis) / access counter (LFU)
  -- counters alongside the value where atomicity matters
```

```
invalidation_log            -- append-only, the correctness backstop
  origin_node, ns, key, epoch, ts
```
```
namespace_config            -- per-namespace policy, versioned
  ns, max_value_bytes, default_ttl_ms, eviction_policy, quota_bytes, compression
```
```
quota / key_counters        -- per-tenant key count and byte usage
```
```
access_buckets              -- sampled hit/miss per namespace for hit-rate SLIs
```

Choices to defend
- **Absolute expiry timestamps, not TTL counters.** Every node then computes the same expiry without coordination, and a restart does not resurrect a key that should have died.
- **Hash tags** (`{user}:profile`) so a multi-key operation lands in one slot; without this, batch operations become cross-slot and Lua scripts fail.
- **Per-namespace configuration** because a cache holding user profiles, HTML fragments, and rate-limit counters needs different max value size, TTL, and eviction.

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Client / Service
   L1: in-process cache (Caffeine/caffeine/go-cache), 1-10 GB per node, 20-30% of hot keys
        | miss (~20-40%)
   L2: regional distributed cache (Redis/Valkey cluster, 3 replicas, 8-16 shards)
        | miss (~1-5%)
   Origin: database (sharded)
        on miss -> read origin -> write L2 -> write L1

Invalidation
   Write to origin -> publish invalidation to Redis pub/sub + append to invalidation_log
   -> every node drops the key from L1 (subscription) and the L2 key is deleted or version-mismatched

Three tiers, one rule: each tier is an optimization over the one below it.
```

Diagram: L1 → L2 → origin, with the invalidation channel running right-to-left.

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Invalidation is the actual problem; consistency is a business decision**

Every cache design is a staleness decision, and you should frame it that way:
- **Cache-aside with TTL-bounded staleness** is the default and is right for most data. The staleness bound is the TTL; if the business can tolerate 5 seconds, this is a complete answer.
- **Explicit invalidation on write** is better but is where the bugs live: the invalidation publish can fail, a subscriber can be disconnected, an L1 node can restart with a stale entry, and messages can be lost. Mitigations: publish **before** and **after** the write, use a **version/epoch** in the value so a stale message cannot resurrect stale data, and keep an append-only invalidation log that nodes can replay on reconnect.
- **Write-through** is only correct if the cache write and the origin write share a transaction boundary, which they do not in general. It is right when the cache *is* the only writer path (for example a counter that the origin reads from the cache).
- **Strong consistency on read** (validate against the origin on every read) defeats the purpose. Almost always the correct answer is: pick a staleness bound per data class, and treat violating it as a bug only for the classes that genuinely matter (permissions, money, inventory).
- **Never cache what you cannot afford to be stale**: authorization decisions, payment state, and inventory must either be read from the origin or versioned with a strict invalidation contract. If someone asks "how do you make the cache consistent with the database," the answer is "you don't, and pretending otherwise is how incidents happen."

**7.2 Eviction under memory pressure**

- **LRU** is simple and has a well-known weakness: a one-time scan of a large dataset evicts everything hot and leaves the cache full of cold items. **SLRU / 2Q** separates the scan-resistant probationary segment from the frequently-used segment and largely fixes this. **LFU with a decayed counter** adapts better to shifting access patterns and is the right choice for object caches.
- **TTL is an eviction policy**: Redis's randomized LRU sampling over a small candidate set (`maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru`) approximates LRU well and is the pragmatic default. Choose `volatile-lru` when every key has a TTL and noeviction when eviction would be catastrophic.
- **Memory-aware policies**: rejecting a value that is too large before it is stored, and compression above ~500 B.
- **Per-namespace quotas** so one namespace cannot evict another's keys. This is the single most useful operational feature and almost nobody designs it in.
- **Cardinality protection**: cap distinct keys per namespace per tenant and return `429` beyond it, because the common failure is an attacker generating a million keys with high cardinality and evicting everything real. The metric to alert on is `keys_per_second` per namespace, not cache size.
- **Warm-up**: after a restart, the cache is empty and every read hits the origin. A warm-up job that replays the top-N keys by historical frequency from the access log prevents a restart from becoming an outage.

**7.3 The thundering herd (cache stampede)**

- **Single-flight / request coalescing**: the first process to miss takes a lock and fetches; all others wait on that result. Implement it at two levels: an in-process coalescer (so 100 goroutines on one pod make one origin call) and a distributed one (a short-lived lock with a fencing token).
- **Probabilistic early expiration (XFetch)**: fetch slightly *before* expiry, with a randomized jitter proportional to the remaining TTL, so a thousand keys expiring at the same instant do not all refresh at the same instant. This is the single highest-leverage fix and it is very cheap.
- **Stagger TTLs**: add jitter to TTLs on write (base × [0.9, 1.1]) so keys populated together do not expire together.
- **Negative caching** with a short TTL for misses, so a popular missing key does not hammer the origin.
- **Hot key replication**: a key that is 20% of traffic is pinned into every L1 in the cluster and served locally.
- **Lock hygiene**: short leases, fencing tokens so a stalled client cannot release another client's lock, and never hold a lock across a slow origin call beyond its lease.

**7.4 Hot keys, skew, and sharding**

- **Shard by key hash** spreads load but concentrates a hot key into one shard. At 100k ops/sec per shard, a key receiving 60k of those is the bottleneck.
- **Fixes in order of cost**: (a) promote the hot key into every L1 and never let it reach L2 — best when the key's value is small and read-mostly; (b) replicate the key across N shards and pick one by requester identity (`hash(userId) % N`), which changes consistency semantics and is fine for counters; (c) move the counter to a local in-memory per-process counter pushed periodically, which removes the hot write path entirely; (d) for a true single-writer workload, use a dedicated shard.
- **Zipfian skew** is the norm for real key distributions, so measure the top-1% of keys' share of traffic. Above ~5% on a single key, treat it as a design problem, not a tuning problem.
- **Consistent hashing with virtual nodes** for shard assignment so adding a shard moves ~1/N of keys, not everything.
- **Read replicas** for read-heavy caches are dangerous: replica lag produces stale reads and replica failover loses cache contents. Prefer a primary with replicas used only as hot standbys, and make losing the cache a non-event.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **Memory before CPU.** Redis shards are single-threaded so throughput is generous; memory ceiling is the real limit, and it is set by key count and value size, not by traffic.
2. **Cardinality explosion** — 300M keys is fine, 3B from one buggy caller is not. Per-namespace key-count caps are the control.
3. **Hot keys/shards** — skew concentrates before the average shows anything.
4. **Eviction thrash** when the working set exceeds memory: hit rate collapses, the origin takes the traffic, and the origin falls over. This is the classic cache-induced outage and it needs a **hit-rate circuit breaker** that bypasses the cache for a class of traffic rather than amplifying it.
5. **Eviction of durable data** — if the cache is being used as a write buffer (read-through cache with no origin copy), eviction is data loss. This is why "cache" must never hold the only copy.
6. **Pub/sub at scale** — invalidation fan-out to 10,000 nodes per write is a hidden thundering herd on the pub/sub layer. Batch and coalesce invalidations.
7. **Cross-region latency** — 5–20 ms makes a remote cache worse than the database. Regional caches plus explicit invalidation, or accept stale reads.
8. **Restart/warm-up** — a cold cache plus no warm-up turns a deploy into an origin overload.
9. **Operational tooling** — `SCAN`-based sampling rather than `KEYS`, memory fragmentation (`activedefrag`), slowlog, and latency histograms per command, because a single `KEYS *` or a large `MGET` can stall a shard.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Recovery |
|---|---|---|
| Whole cache down | all reads go to the origin | **the origin must survive a 3-5× read spike**; circuit breaker, read-replica offload, serve stale from L1 |
| One shard down | ~1/N of keys miss | shard-level failover; client-side retry to another replica |
| Memory pressure / eviction storm | hit rate collapses | raise maxmemory, shed cold namespaces, disable caching for a noisy class |
| Invalidation channel down | L1 entries stale until TTL | short TTLs for critical namespaces; version tokens so stale messages cannot apply |
| Cardinality attack | eviction storm | per-namespace key caps → `429`; ban the caller |
| Cache stampede | origin QPS spike | single-flight, XFetch, TTL jitter |
| Replication lag on a replica read | stale reads | serve from the primary for correctness-sensitive keys |
| Failover loses cache contents | cold cache; origin spike | warm-up job; pre-seeded replicas |
| Slow command blocks a shard | p99 explodes on one shard | latency histograms, command allowlists, `SLOWLOG`, memory fragmentation management |
| Poisoned value in cache | persistent wrong data | versioned values, a namespace-wide purge endpoint, and short TTLs on risky namespaces |
| Clock skew causing early/late expiry | keys dying early or living too long | absolute expiry from the store's clock; NTP |
| Client bug caching a response under the wrong key | permanent wrong data until TTL | key includes all dimensions (version, locale, tenant) — this is a schema error, fix the key |
| Primary cache region loss | global cache unavailable | regional caches serve; accept bounded staleness rather than blocking |

Invariant: **cache loss is always survivable.** If that is not true of a given key, it is not a cache entry.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Topology | regional caches + in-process L1 | one global cache | cross-region is 5–20 ms, worse than the origin |
| Consistency | TTL-bounded staleness + invalidation | read-through validation | validation per read defeats the cache |
| Expiry | absolute timestamps | decrementing TTL counters | no coordination, restart-safe |
| Eviction | LFU/SLRU + TTL + per-namespace quotas | pure LRU | scan resistance and namespace isolation |
| Refresh | XFetch + TTL jitter + single-flight | refresh on expiry | prevents the stampede |
| Hot keys | promote into L1, replicate counters | hope for the hash to spread | Zipfian skew concentrates |
| Write path | cache-aside | write-through | write-through implies a transaction you do not have |
| Persistence | optional, best-effort | required durability | the origin is the source of truth |
| Negative caching | short TTL | pass misses through | a 404 storm must not reach the origin |
| Locks | single-flight + fencing token | naive SETNX | stale holders release others' locks |
| Inter-node invalidation | pub/sub + version tokens + replayable log | TTL only | TTL-only means seconds of stale L1 data |
| Bypass switch | per-class circuit breaker | none | the cache must be able to protect the origin from itself |

Rejected wholesale
- **Using the cache as a write buffer / materialized view.** Now eviction is data loss and availability becomes the cache's problem.
- **Redis with `maxmemory-policy noeviction` and a big instance.** Cheap until it isn't; one memory spike returns errors to every caller.
- **Storing large values (MBs) in the cache.** One key can evict a hundred thousand small ones; put big blobs in object storage and cache the pointer.
- **A single global cache "because it is simpler."** Fails on latency, on blast radius, and on region-level availability.
- **Adding a third tier before measuring L1/L2 hit rates.** The answer is almost always that L1 needs tuning, not another network hop.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "How do you handle a key expiring in 60 seconds and getting 50k requests a second?"**
Model answer: This is the classic stampede and the fix is probabilistic early refresh, not locking. Instead of fetching when the key is expired, each reader fetches when the remaining TTL is below `XFetch(ttl, requestsPerSecond)`, which is roughly the time it would take to fetch the value. So at 50k requests/second the XFetch threshold becomes very small and readers refresh proactively, and the threshold is randomized per request so 50k readers do not all decide to refresh at the same instant. I also jitter TTLs on write, so keys populated together do not expire together, and I coalesce in-process with single-flight so 200 goroutines in one pod produce one origin call. If it is still a problem, the right answer for a very hot key is to stop caching it in the shared tier: promote it into every local L1 so it never leaves the process, or, if it is a counter, replicate it across N shards and pick one by requester identity. The thing to avoid is a distributed lock around every refresh, because the lock becomes the next bottleneck and a stalled holder makes things worse. The one requirement that must hold is that the refresh must be idempotent and cheap, since it will happen far more often than the TTL suggests.

**Q2. "How do you decide what to evict?"**
Model answer: Memory is the constraint, not CPU, so eviction policy is where the design effort goes. Plain LRU has a well-known pathology: one bulk scan of a large table evicts everything actually hot and refills the cache with data nobody will read again. So I would use LFU with a logarithmic decayed counter, or SLRU/2Q with a probationary segment for the first access and a protected segment for repeats, both of which are scan-resistant and adapt to shifting access patterns. Second, TTL is itself an eviction policy — randomized sampling over a small candidate set is a good approximation and is what every production Redis does. Third, and most operationally important, per-namespace quotas: a user-profile namespace and an HTML-fragment namespace and a counter namespace have very different value sizes and lifetimes, and one noisy namespace must not be able to evict another's keys. That single feature prevents most cache-related incidents. Fourth, guard the two failure modes directly: reject oversized values before storing them, and cap distinct keys per namespace per tenant, because the common outage is a caller generating millions of keys and evicting everything real. And I would alert on `keys_per_second` per namespace rather than on cache size, because the failure arrives before the size does.

**Q3. "How do you keep the cache consistent with the database?"**
Model answer: I would not, and that is the honest engineering position — the cache is eventually consistent with a staleness bound, and the design question is what that bound is per data class. For most read-mostly data, cache-aside with a 5–60 second TTL is a complete and defensible answer: reads may be up to the TTL stale, and nothing else is required. Where staleness is unacceptable — permissions, money, inventory — I do not cache-and-trust; I either read through to the origin or I attach a version token to the value and reject anything whose version is older than the current one. Explicit invalidation on write improves the bound but does not eliminate the failure modes: the publish can fail, a subscriber can be disconnected, an L1 node can restart holding a stale entry, and messages can be reordered. So I publish around the write, stamp values with an epoch so a late-arriving stale invalidation cannot resurrect stale data, and keep an append-only invalidation log that a reconnecting node replays. The mistake I would warn against is trying to make reads strongly consistent by validating against the origin every time — that is just the database with extra latency and less durability.

**Q4. "The cache is slow. What do you look at?"**
Model answer: Latency in a single-threaded shard is almost never compute, so I look at what blocks the event loop. First, `SLOWLOG` and the latency histogram per command, to find the slow command class — a `KEYS *`, a very large `MGET`, or a `SORT` will stall a shard entirely. Second, large values: a single MB value means synchronous allocation and copies on every access, so I check the value-size distribution per namespace and move big blobs to object storage with only the pointer cached. Third, memory pressure and fragmentation — as RSS grows but the keyspace does not, defragmentation is the issue. Fourth, network: a slow client with a large output buffer blocks the shard, so I look at output-buffer limits per connection. Fifth, CPU saturation from an unexpected command mix, often a sudden rise in `SCAN`-heavy traffic or a hot key hammering one shard. Sixth, cross-AZ placement — if the client and shard are in different availability zones the round trip doubles. And seventh, the connection pool: too few connections causes client-side queuing that looks exactly like cache slowness. The fastest triage is per-shard latency plus per-command latency plus memory, and the first question is always whether one shard or all of them.

**Q5. "Someone says the cache caused the database outage. Explain."**
Model answer: That is the canonical cache-induced outage and it has a specific shape. The working set grew slightly past the memory ceiling, so eviction started evicting hot keys, so the hit rate collapsed, so every read went to the database, and the database — which was sized for a 95% cache hit rate — saw a 20× read spike and fell over. The compounding factor is usually a cold start: a restart or deploy empties the cache, there is no warm-up, and the same thing happens immediately. There are three defences and I would want all three. One, a hit-rate circuit breaker: when the hit rate drops below a threshold, bypass the cache for a class of traffic — serving stale data from L1 is better than amplifying into the origin — and shed rather than forward. Two, the origin must be sized and provisioned to survive a 3–5× read spike, because the cache's absence is a planned-for event, not an edge case; that means read replicas and rate limiting on top of the cache. Three, a warm-up job that replays the top-N keys by historical access frequency after a restart, so a deploy is not an outage. And on top of those, per-namespace quotas and cardinality caps, because the way you usually get into this state is a caller generating keys nobody else reads.

**Q6. "How would you migrate from one cache to another, or add a second tier?"**
Model answer: I would not do a big-bang migration, and the ordering matters more than the technology. If the goal is a new L2, I run it shadow-first: the new cache is populated from the same reads but never served from, and I compare hit rate, value staleness, and latency distributions over a few days. The comparison that catches real bugs is a value-equality check on overlapping keys, because a different serialization or TTL policy will show up there and nowhere else. Then I shift a small percentage of reads — 1%, then 10%, then 50% — with an automatic rollback on error rate or hit-rate regression, and per-key-type rather than per-namespace where possible so a bad value class is contained. During the cutover the old cache stays warm and serving, because a cold old cache means the fallback path is a database stampede if the new one wobbles. For an L1 added in front of the existing L2, the same approach applies but the tuning question is different: L1 is per-process with a fixed memory budget, so I would start small, measure the L1 hit rate per service, and be prepared to find that some services have hot keys too large for an L1 to be worth it. Either way, the correctness requirement is unchanged — version tokens in the values and an invalidation channel that both tiers subscribe to, so adding a tier never creates a third source of staleness.

---

## 12.5.19 Metrics and logging system

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design a metrics and logging platform. Every service emits metrics and logs; we need dashboards, alerting, log search, and to keep the bill survivable."

The score is in: **volume arithmetic** (this is a data-pipeline design), the write path for agents, cardinality as the killer, the difference between metrics and logs, and what gets dropped under load.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Metrics: counters, gauges, histograms, summaries, exemplars, tags.
- F2. Logs: structured, multi-line, searchable, with retention tiers.
- F3. Traces: distributed tracing with context propagation.
- F4. Dashboards, ad-hoc query, alerting rules with routing.
- F5. Cardinality limits and per-tenant quotas.
- F6. Retention policies per metric and log stream.
- F7. Backfill, rollups, and downsampling.

Non-functional
- N1. Ingest **5M metrics/sec** and **1M log lines/sec** sustained; 10× burst.
- N2. Metric write-to-queryable latency: **< 60 s** for dashboards, **< 10 s** for alerting.
- N3. Log search latency **< 5 s** over 30 days.
- N4. Cost-bounded: sampling policies and retention must be explicit design decisions.
- N5. Lossy by design under overload: **drop before you cascade**.
- N6. Multi-tenant isolation of cost and quota.

---

**3. Estimation**

Volume
- 5M metrics/sec × ~120 B = **600 MB/s = 52 TB/day raw**.
- 1M log lines/sec × 400 B = **400 MB/s = 34 TB/day**.
- Storage after compression (~10×) and rollups: metrics ~1 TB/day at 15 months, logs ~3 TB/day at 30 days.

Cost arithmetic that must be stated
- At $0.02/GB-month, 52 TB/day raw = 1.5 PB/month = **$30k/month** raw, ~$3k/month compressed. Logs at 34 TB/day = 1 PB/month = **$20k/month** compressed, **$400k/month** raw. **Log retention is the entire cost story**, which is why sampling and tiering are architecture, not configuration.

Cardinality — the real killer
- 1,000 instances × 50 metric series × 20 tag dimensions of moderate cardinality = **1M+ active series per metric name**, and Prometheus-style stores fall over well before 10M.
- The rule: **tags must be low-cardinality by construction.** Never tag with user_id, request_id, email, raw path, or error message. Use exemplars to link a metric spike to traces, and traces to logs.
- Budget explicitly: `max_series_per_metric` and per-team quotas; a new tag that explodes cardinality should be rejected by a cardinality analyzer in CI.

Arithmetic to state
- "One high-cardinality tag — say `path` — turns 1,000 series into 1,000,000, and that is the single most common way a metrics pipeline dies. Cardinality limits belong in the SDK and in CI, not in a dashboard."

---

**4. API design**

```
POST /v1/metrics      (protobuf/snappy over HTTP or gRPC)
  MetricPoint[] { name, type COUNTER|GAUGE|HISTOGRAM, value,
                  labels{env,service,instance,route,status}, ts, exemplars[] }

POST /v1/logs         (OTLP/JSON)
  { "resource": {"service.name":"orders","k8s.pod.name":"..."},
    "severity":"ERROR", "body":"...", "traceId":"...", "spanId":"...",
    "ts":"...", "attributes": {...} }

GET /v1/query?metric=...&labels=...&from=&to=&step=      -> series
POST /v1/query_range  (PromQL-like)  -> matrix
POST /v1/search/logs  { query:"level:ERROR service:orders AND msg:\"timeout\"", from,to }
POST /v1/alert/rules  { expr, for, labels, routes[] }
GET  /v1/cardinality?name=...  -> series count by label, with cost estimate
```
Errors
| Case | Response | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Cardinality exceeded | 400 with the offending label | fail the write, alert the owner |
| Value too large / label too long | 400 | |
| Series limit per metric | 429 `series_limit_exceeded` | **must be enforced** or one team breaks everyone |
| Quota exceeded | 429 with the quota and reset | |
| Out-of-order sample | 202, drop with a metric | bounded out-of-order window |
| Timestamp too old | 400 | retention boundary |
| Log line too large (1 MB) | truncate + flag | |
| Backend unavailable | 202 accepted + local buffer | **never block the emitting service** |

---

**5. Data model**

```
series (metrics)
  name, labels MAP, label_values_hash, first_seen, last_seen, type
  -- a series is identified by the full label set; the label set is stored once
  samples: (series_hash, ts) -> value    -- columnar, time-partitioned, downsampled
```
```
exemplars
  series_hash, ts, trace_id, span_id      -- ~1% of samples, the metric→trace bridge
```
```
logs (columnar, partitioned by service and day)
  ts, service, severity, body, attributes MAP, trace_id, span_id
  -- inverted index on selected attributes; full-text index on body
```
```
traces (spans)
  trace_id, span_id, parent_span_id, service, name, start_ns, duration_ns,
  attributes, events, status
  -- indexed by trace_id for tree reconstruction
```
```
retention / rollup policy
  metric: raw 15d -> 5m rollup 90d -> 1h rollup 15mo
  log:   full 7d -> sampled-indexed 30d -> cold object storage 90d
```
```
alert_rules  expr, evaluation_interval, for_window, route, mute_windows, runbook_url
```

---

**6. High-level design**

```
All services (SDK: OTel + Prometheus client)
  metrics --push--> Collector fleet (agent on node, autoscaled, sharded by service)
  logs    --push--> Collector fleet
  traces  --push--> Collector fleet
       |
       v
  Ingest (Kafka as the buffer: durable, replayable, absorbs 10x bursts)
       |
       +--> Metrics path: compaction -> TSDB (time-partitioned, downsampled)
       +--> Logs path:    parse/normalize -> columnar store + index
       |                   -> object storage (cold, cheap)
       +--> Traces:       trace store with tail sampling
       +--> Hot-tier:     last 15 min in Redis/in-memory for dashboards
       |
       v
  Query API (fan-out by tenant/time range) -> Grafana / ad-hoc / alerting
  Alert evaluator -> routing (PagerDuty/Slack) with grouping and suppression

Principle: the emitting service must never block. If the pipeline is unhealthy,
drop or buffer locally and report that it did.
```

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Cardinality: the number one failure mode**

- Enforce limits in three places: the SDK (per-metric label allowlist), the ingest (per-metric series cap returning 429), and CI (a cardinality analyzer that fails a deploy adding a high-cardinality label).
- Give each team a **series budget** per metric name, visible in a dashboard, because shared infrastructure with shared cost needs quotas.
- Ban specific anti-patterns by policy and by tooling: user_id, request_id, session_id, full URL paths, raw error messages, email, and unbounded enums.
- Use **exemplars** to connect a metric spike to specific traces, then traces to logs. This is the correct answer to "I need to see the individual requests behind this p99" and it removes the temptation to add a request_id tag.
- Route-level aggregation is the fix for path cardinality: use a **route template** (`/orders/:id`) as the label, set by the framework, with the raw path only in logs/traces.
- Alert on cardinality growth itself: new-series-per-minute per metric is a leading indicator of an impending incident.

**7.2 Lossy under load, in a defined order**

When the pipeline is behind, drop in this order, and document it as a policy:
1. **Debug/trace-level logs** first — the highest volume, lowest value.
2. **Trace spans**, via tail sampling: keep all errors and all slow traces, sample the successful fast ones at 1–5%. Keep 100% of errors; that is the whole argument for tail sampling over head sampling.
3. **High-cardinality metrics** and per-instance breakdowns — roll them up.
4. **Internal high-frequency metrics** from libraries (GC, queue depth per-pod).
5. **Application logs at ERROR and above** — dropped last, if ever.
6. **Never drop**: alerting-critical SLO metrics, audit logs, security events.
- Buffer locally with a bounded disk queue when the collector is unreachable, so a brief ingest outage does not create a data gap in the metrics.
- The local buffer must be bounded and drop-oldest, or a long outage fills the disk and takes down the host.

**7.3 Logs: the cost problem**

- **Sample by default** for high-volume successes. Keep 100% of errors and a fraction of successes — usually 1–5%. This alone is a 20–100× cost reduction.
- **Structure at the emitter**: JSON with a small, controlled attribute set, not arbitrary key-value soup. An unconstrained attribute map is how log storage explodes.
- **Index selectively**: index `service`, `severity`, `trace_id`, and a handful of promoted fields; full-text index the body only if the full-text cost is justified, and consider it optional per namespace.
- **Tier retention**: 7 days full-fidelity hot, 30 days with sampled indexing, 90 days in cold object storage with an on-demand "rehydrate this query" job. Cold query latency of minutes is fine for an incident investigation a day later.
- **Drop noisy successes**: 200/`health` per second from 1,000 instances is 200k lines/sec of information-free volume. Sample them to a fixed per-interval budget.
- **Redact at the emitter**, not at ingest: PII and secrets must never leave the process, because the store has broader access and longer retention.

**7.4 Query, alerting, and cost ergonomics**

- **Fan-out query**: shard by tenant and time; parallelize and merge. A dashboard with 20 panels from 20 services is 20 queries, so caching per-panel results and aligning time windows to a shared step matters more than raw query speed.
- **Recompute-resistant rollups**: precompute 5-minute and 1-hour rollups so a 90-day dashboard does not scan raw data.
- **Alerting must be robust**: evaluate against the hot tier with a `for` window to avoid flapping, group and suppress related alerts (a failing dependency should not page 30 teams), route by ownership labels, and require a runbook link. Alert on **symptoms users feel** (SLO burn) more than on causes (CPU).
- **Cardinality-aware cost attribution**: every team can see the storage and query cost of their own metrics and logs. This is the only thing that actually controls the bill at scale; a dashboard nobody looks at does not.
- **Trace-to-metrics-to-logs correlation** is the core workflow: SLO burn alert → exemplar → trace → log lines. Build the links in the schema (trace_id in both) and make the UI do the hopping.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **Kafka buffer depth** — the shock absorber for 10× bursts. Needs retention and lag-based backpressure to the emitters (drop, not block).
2. **Hot-tier memory** — the last 15 minutes of all series in memory. At 5M series this is the sizing constraint; use a ring buffer with downsampling on write.
3. **Ingest cost** — every series write is a sort/merge cost; compaction is CPU-bound and scales with series count, not sample count.
4. **Log index size** — a full-text index over 30 days of logs is enormous; selective indexing is mandatory.
5. **Query fan-out cost** — a dashboard over 15 months of raw data will kill the query tier; rollups are the only answer.
6. **Cardinality explosion** — always the first thing to break; limits must exist before it happens.
7. **Ingest-node failure** — the emitters' local buffers and Kafka replay handle it.
8. **Tenant isolation of cost** — without quotas, one team's debug logging consumes the budget.
9. **Clock skew** across hosts producing out-of-order samples; bounded out-of-order windows and store-side timestamps.
10. **Storage growth vs retention enforcement** — deleting data is a background job that competes with ingest.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Invariant |
|---|---|---|
| Collector down | emitters buffer locally, bounded, drop-oldest | the emitting service never blocks |
| Kafka down | emitters buffer then drop by priority | alerting metrics kept longest |
| Metrics store degraded | dashboards fall back to the hot tier; alerting uses coarse pre-aggregates | alerting continues |
| Log store degraded | log search unavailable; **metrics and traces continue** | partial degradation by tier |
| Cardinality explosion | new series rejected with 429 | existing series keep working |
| Query tier down | dashboards show cached panels | read-only experience |
| Clock skew | out-of-order samples dropped | bounded, counted |
| Retention job behind | storage cost rises | alert on it |
| Alert evaluator down | no paging | runbook; on-call is blind, so this pages |
| Reindex/compaction backlog | disk usage rises, query latency rises | compaction is online; keep it ahead |
| A single tenant floods logs | quota 429s that tenant | other tenants unaffected |
| Trace store full | tail sampling rate falls | errors always retained |

Invariant: **telemetry must never affect the service that produces it.** Every path buffers or drops; nothing blocks.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ingest | push with local buffers | pull/scrape | fewer failure modes at scale; no per-target config management |
| Buffer | Kafka | direct writes to the store | absorbs 10× bursts and enables replay |
| Cardinality | enforced limits + exemplars | unlimited tags | the standard way this pipeline dies |
| Sampling | tail sampling on traces, errors always | head sampling | keeps the traces you actually need |
| Logs | sampled, structured, selective indexing | full-fidelity 90 days | 20–100× cost reduction |
| Retention | tiered with cold storage | single hot tier | cost |
| Query | precomputed rollups | raw scans for any range | 90-day dashboards are impossible otherwise |
| Alerting | SLO burn + grouping + runbooks | paging on every cause metric | signal quality |
| Multi-tenancy | per-team budgets + quotas | shared unlimited pool | cost control requires attribution |
| PII | redacted at the emitter | redacted at ingest | the store has broader access and longer retention |

Rejected wholesale
- **No retention policy.** Storage cost grows without bound and you discover it on an invoice.
- **Sampling errors.** Every serious incident needs them.
- **Full-fidelity logs.** One team at DEBUG level can cost more than everything else combined.
- **A relational database for time series.** Wrong write pattern, wrong compression, wrong query model.
- **Paging on CPU.** Cause metrics page for things users do not feel.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "Metrics are ingesting fine but dashboards got slow. Why?"**
Model answer: In order of likelihood, cardinality. A new label — a route template that became a raw path, or an error code or a region list — multiplies the active series count by an order of magnitude, and the metrics store pays for that on every write, not just on query. Series count is the number I check first, and the per-metric series growth rate is the leading indicator. Second, series churn: many short-lived label values (a pod name, an ephemeral instance id) create constant new series, which stresses compaction and the index even when the total count is stable. Fix is a stable `instance` label derived from the pod UID and not from anything that changes on restart. Third, query shape: a dashboard that scans 15 months of raw data, or a high-cardinality label used in a `GROUP BY`. The fix is rollups and enforcing a maximum time range per panel. Fourth, retention enforcement falling behind so the hot tier grows. Fifth, compaction backlog from a reindex. I would also check whether the query tier and the hot tier are separate and whether hot-tier memory is sized for the series count, because a hot tier that is too small silently evicts the data you are graphing.

**Q2. "What's your biggest storage cost, and what would you cut first?"**
Model answer: Logs, by a wide margin. A million lines/second at 400 bytes is 34 TB/day, roughly a terabyte per month after compression, and if anyone asks for 90 days of full-fidelity everything else becomes irrelevant. The first cut is sampling successes — keeping 100% of errors and warnings while sampling successful requests to 1–5% is typically a 20–100× reduction with almost no diagnostic loss, because when you are debugging you are nearly always following an error or a slow trace. Second is the retention shape: 7 days hot, 30 days with a sampled index, 90 days in cold object storage with an on-demand rehydrate job. Third is noisy successes — 200 health checks/second from 1,000 instances is 200k lines/second carrying no information. Fourth is attribute discipline: an unconstrained key-value map in log lines is how log storage quietly multiplies. Metrics are usually the smaller bill, and I would cut their cardinality before I would cut their resolution. The change I refuse is cutting errors, which is why retention tiers and per-tenant budgets come before any global reduction.

**Q3. "Ingest is 3× over capacity. What do you drop?"**
Model answer: A defined priority order, and the order is the design. First debug-level logs, which are the highest volume and the lowest value. Second trace spans, using tail sampling — keep 100% of errors and slow traces, sample the fast successful ones. Third per-instance metric breakdowns and per-pod library metrics, rolled up to the service level. Fourth high-frequency internal metrics. Errors at ERROR and above are last, and audit and security logs are never dropped. Meanwhile the pipeline itself must not become the outage: collectors buffer locally in a bounded, drop-oldest disk queue, and Kafka absorbs the burst so the emitters never block on the store. That last point is the invariant I would protect above everything else — telemetry must not be able to take down the service producing it. And I would put a visible "we are dropping X" indicator on the dashboards, because a silent partial dataset is worse than a known partial one: people will draw conclusions from numbers that were never collected.

**Q4. "How do you keep a single metric from killing the cluster?"**
Model answer: Cardinality limits enforced in three places so there is no single point of enforcement. In the SDK, a per-metric label allowlist, so a high-cardinality tag cannot be emitted at all. At ingest, a per-metric series cap that returns 429 once the limit is hit, which is where you stop one team from evicting everyone else. In CI, a cardinality analyzer that fails the deploy when a new label would push a metric past a threshold, which is the only place you can prevent it rather than detect it. On top of that, every team gets a series budget and a cost view of their own metrics, because shared infrastructure with shared cost needs quotas or it will grow until it breaks. And for legitimate needs that require per-request identification, the answer is exemplars rather than tags: attach a trace id to roughly 1% of samples for a histogram bucket, which gives you a direct hop from a metric spike to specific traces to specific log lines. That replaces the temptation to tag with a request id, which is the single most destructive thing anyone can add to a metrics pipeline.

**Q5. "How do you debug a production incident at 2 a.m.?"**
Model answer: Start from the symptom in SLO terms, not from a host or a dashboard. The burn-rate alert tells me which SLO is burning and how fast; that scopes the blast radius. Then metrics by service to find where latency or error rate changed, using exemplars to jump from the offending histogram bucket to real traces — that immediately tells me whether it is slow, erroring, or a specific dependency. Then the trace view for one exemplar trace shows the full span tree with per-span timing, which localizes it to a service and often to a specific call. Then logs for that trace id, which has the request-level detail — the SQL statement, the cache key, the error message — because trace ids propagate into logs, so I can pull the exact lines for one request rather than searching a namespace. That chain from metric to trace to log is the workflow, and its existence is a schema decision (exemplars, and trace id in both traces and logs) made long before the incident. What I would not do is start by SSHing into a box, because that path finds a symptom's cause only when the cause is on that box. And if the chain breaks — no exemplars for that metric, or logs without trace ids — that is a design gap I would fix afterwards while it is fresh.

**Q6. "How would you migrate this platform to a new backend?"**
Model answer: Shadow first, then canary, and treat the query API as the stable contract. The ingest format and the query API are the interface everyone actually depends on, so I would freeze those first and let the backends change behind them. Step one is dual-write from the collectors to both backends, with the new one in shadow — populated, never queried. That gives me a direct value-level comparison: same series, same timestamps, same rollups, and any divergence is a schema or semantics bug I find without user impact. Step two is shadow-read: a small percentage of queries goes to the new backend and results are diffed, which catches differences in downsampling, rate functions, and label matching semantics — the things that differ between implementations even when the schema is identical. Step three is progressive cutover by tenant, 1% then 10% then 50%, with an automatic rollback on error rate or query-latency regression. The old backend stays warm and complete throughout, because during cutover I need a fallback that is not a cold start; a cold fallback under load is a database-style stampede against the ingest tier. And I would migrate metrics, logs, and traces as separate projects with separate timelines, because they have different data models and a shared migration is how both get stuck.

---

## 12.5.20 Feature flag service

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

---

**1. The question as an interviewer asks it**

> "Design a feature flag service. Thousands of flags, evaluated in the request path of every service, targeted by user and percentage, and it must never take down production when the flag service has a bad day."

The score is in: **local caching with a hard staleness bound**, **evaluation speed**, **safe rollout and kill switches**, **flag lifecycle hygiene**, and **the fact that a flag service failure must fail *open* to the default**.

---

**2. Requirements**

Functional
- F1. Boolean and variant (multi-value) flags with typed values.
- F2. Targeting rules: user, cohort, segment, percentage rollout, geo, app version, device.
- F3. Percentage rollouts that are **sticky** (a user always gets the same variant).
- F4. Kill switches with a global, instant disable.
- F5. Lifecycle: create → ramp → default on → retire, with owner and expiry.
- F6. Audit log of every change with actor, reason, and time.
- F7. Local/server-side evaluation SDKs for many languages.

Non-functional
- N1. Evaluation **< 1 ms**, and normally **zero network calls** (local cache).
- N2. Flag changes visible everywhere within **< 5 s**.
- N3. Availability: a flag service outage must **degrade to defaults**, not take down the caller.
- N4. Storage small: 5,000 flags × 1 KB = 5 MB — trivially replicated and cached.
- N5. Correctness: the same user must never flap between variants.
- N6. Rollback must be instant and safe.

---

**3. Estimation**

Scale
- 5,000 flags, 500 services, 100M client instances.
- Evaluations: every request consults a handful of flags → **~500M evaluations/sec** across the fleet. This is the number that makes "call the service per evaluation" obviously wrong.
- Configuration size: 5,000 flags × 1 KB = **5 MB**. Even at 100 KB per service's flag set, that fits in memory everywhere.
- Change volume: ~50 flag changes/hour peak → trivial ingest.

Arithmetic to state
- "500 million evaluations a second against 5 MB of configuration. The only viable design is push the whole config to every process and evaluate locally. The service is a configuration distribution system, not a request-path service. Its availability requirement is near-zero because it is not on the request path."

---

**4. API design**

```
GET  /v1/flags/{key}?context=<json>      -> for evaluation (fallback path only)
{ "key":"checkout_v2", "type":"VARIANT", "variants":["control","treatment"],
  "defaultVariant":"control", "rules":[...], "version": 18422, "hashSeed": "a3f1" }

PUT  /v1/flags/{key}                    { "value":..., "rules":[...], "version": 18423 }
-> 200   (optimistic concurrency on `version`)
POST /v1/flags/{key}:kill               -> 202   (global override, evaluated locally, < 1s everywhere)
POST /v1/flags/{key}:ramp  { "percentage": 5, "stages": [1,5,25,50,100], "autoRollback": {...} }
GET  /v1/flags?owner=&stale=&expiring=   -> lifecycle hygiene view
```

Evaluation context
```json
{ "userId":"u_9f3c", "deviceId":"d_1", "appVersion":"8.2.1", "geo":"in",
  "attributes": { "plan":"pro", "betaCohort":"internal" } }
```
Local SDK result
```
{ "value":"treatment", "variantIndex":1, "reason":"rule_3",
  "flagVersion":18422, "source":"cache", "staleMs": 1400 }
```
Errors
| Case | Response |
|---|---|
| Unknown flag key | SDK returns the compiled default; API 404 |
| Version conflict on write | 409 with the current version |
| Evaluation context missing `userId` for a percentage flag | SDK logs and returns the default variant, does not flap |
| Flag service unreachable | SDK serves last-known config; after `maxStaleMs`, use compiled defaults |
| Malformed rule | reject at write time, never at evaluation time |

The `version` field for optimistic concurrency and the `hashSeed` for sticky bucketing are both required.

---

**5. Data model**

```
flags
  key           TEXT PRIMARY KEY
  type          ENUM(BOOLEAN, VARIANT, NUMBER, JSON)
  default_value JSONB
  variants      JSONB
  rules         JSONB        -- ordered; first match wins
  version       BIGINT
  owner         TEXT
  expires_at    TIMESTAMPTZ NULL     -- enforced retirement
  tags          TEXT[]
  state         ENUM(ACTIVE, RAMPING, KILLED, RETIRED)
```
```
flag_audit               -- append-only, immutable
  flag_key, version, old_value, new_value, actor, reason, ts, ip
```
```
evaluation_config        -- the artifact pushed to every process
  { "flags": {...}, "killSwitches": {...}, "version": 18422, "generatedAt": ...,
    "checksum": "sha256:..." }
```
```
rollout_state
  flag_key, stage_index, percentage, started_at, auto_rollback_metric, auto_rollback_threshold
```

---

**6. High-level design**

```
Admin UI / API  ->  Flag Store (Postgres, strong)  ->  Change Bus (Kafka)
                                                      |
                        +-----------------------------+-----------------------------+
                        v                                                         v
                Config Builder (renders the full        |                     Push service
                evaluation config, signs a checksum)   |                     (websocket/long-poll
                        |                               |                      per SDK group)
                        v                               v
              Object storage / CDN <--- SDKs fetch or receive push, verify checksum
                        |                               |
                        +---------------+---------------+
                                        v
                     In-process evaluation cache (every process, every language)
                     evaluates locally, < 1 ms, zero network calls
```

The diagram's key message: **the flag service is a publisher, and every process is a subscriber holding the whole config in memory.**

---

**7. Deep dives**

**7.1 Evaluation: fast, deterministic, and never blocking**

- **Local evaluation only.** All targeting rules, percentage rollouts, and kill switches are evaluated in-process. No network call in the request path, ever.
- **Percentage rollouts must be sticky**: `bucket = hash(flagKey + hashSeed + userId) % 10000`. Deterministic hashing means a user never flaps, and including the flag key prevents correlated bucketing across flags. The `hashSeed` lets you re-randomize deliberately (for a re-run of an experiment) rather than accidentally.
- **Performance**: pre-compile rules into a flat structure at config load. A linear scan over a handful of ordered rules is nanoseconds; the cost is entirely in config parsing, which happens once per version.
- **Evaluation must be total**: it returns a value for every input, including malformed contexts. Missing `userId` on a percentage flag returns the default variant, not a random one, so nobody flaps.
- **Counts and metrics as a side effect**: sample 1% of evaluations into the pipeline for exposure metrics (is the treatment actually being served?), with **exposure logging at assignment time, not at conversion time** — a common and expensive mistake in experimentation.
- **Type safety**: compile flags into the language's native type at load, so a flag that returns the wrong type fails at load rather than in production.

**7.2 Distribution, staleness, and the failure mode that matters**

- **Push, not pull.** SDKs hold a long-lived subscription (websocket) plus a periodic full fetch as a backstop. A change is visible in **< 1 s** in the common case.
- **Checksums and signatures**: the config artifact carries a version and a checksum; the SDK rejects a corrupt or partial config and keeps the last good one. Never accept a config you cannot verify.
- **Failure policy, stated precisely**: on connectivity loss, serve last-known-good config up to `maxStaleMs` (minutes to hours depending on the flag's risk), then fall back to **compiled-in defaults**. The SDK ships with the flag's default value baked in, so a first-ever process start with no network is correct.
- **Fail open to the safe default**, which is a per-flag decision recorded at definition time. For most flags the default is `false`; for a kill switch the default is "not killed"; for a genuinely safety-critical flag the default must be the conservative path.
- **Never block on flags.** SDK evaluation is synchronous and local, with a hard CPU budget (tens of microseconds) and no I/O, no logging on the hot path beyond sampling, and no allocation-heavy rule evaluation.
- **Config size matters**: 5,000 flags × 1 KB is fine, but a rule set that bloats to 10 MB makes startup slow on mobile clients. Cap flag count per SDK instance and support namespacing so a mobile app ships only the flags it uses.

**7.3 Rollout safety and the kill switch**

- **Progressive rollout with automatic rollback**: stages (1 → 5 → 25 → 50 → 100), each gated on a metric with a threshold and a time window. This is the single most valuable feature, because it is the difference between a bad rollout costing 1% of traffic and costing 100%.
- **The kill switch is separate and simpler**: a global boolean that overrides all targeting and is evaluated first in the local SDK. It must be **< 1 s everywhere**, which is why it rides the push channel rather than a stored flag evaluation.
- **Rollback safety**: rollback must never itself be an outage. Two rules: the flag's default must be the safe state, and variant changes must be backward-compatible (add a variant rather than renaming one), because a client that cached config version N must not break when the server moves to N+1.
- **Config versioning and client compatibility**: the config carries a minimum client version; clients too old to understand a variant fall back to the default. This is the same discipline as API versioning and it prevents a "just a small change" from breaking old app builds.
- **Audit everything**: who changed what, when, why, from where, with the previous and new value. Flag changes are production changes and should be reviewed with the same seriousness as a deploy.

**7.4 Lifecycle hygiene — the thing that actually causes pain**

- **Flag debt is the real cost.** Thousands of stale flags mean long evaluation times, confusing behavior, and an ever-growing risk surface.
- **Every flag requires an owner and an expiry date** at creation time. This is enforced by the API, not by a wiki.
- **Automatic retirement**: at expiry, the system either deletes the flag or flips it to its default and files a PR. The default path must be *tested* — flipping a flag to default changes behavior, and that behavior needs to be known.
- **Two hard rules worth stating**: never reuse a flag key for a different purpose (a cached client keeps the old semantics and you will get a bug you cannot reproduce), and never nest two flags controlling the same behavior (the interaction matrix is untestable).
- **CI integration**: a linter that fails a deploy adding an unowned or unexpiring flag, and a weekly report of flags at 100% rollout for more than 14 days, which is the "should this be deleted?" queue.
- **Testing**: every flag's default path is exercised in integration tests, and there should be a test that flips a flag and asserts behavior, otherwise the flag is untested code that ships to production.

---

**8. Scaling and bottlenecks**

1. **Evaluation CPU** at 500M evaluations/sec across the fleet. Fixes: flat pre-compiled rules, microsecond budgets, and fewer flags evaluated per request.
2. **Config size on clients**, especially mobile. Fixes: namespacing, per-SDK flag sets, size caps with alerts.
3. **Push fan-out** to every process and every mobile instance. Fixes: grouped subscriptions, so one change fans out to a few hundred gateway nodes rather than a million sockets; CDN-hosted config artifacts for clients that cannot hold a socket.
4. **Config propagation latency** for the kill switch. Fixes: dedicated priority channel for kill switches, verified by a synthetic check from several regions.
5. **Startup cost** for a process booting with 5,000 flags. Fixes: compile at build time for known flags; ship the compiled defaults in the binary.
6. **Rule complexity** — a flag with 50 targeting conditions is a performance and comprehension problem. Cap rule count and require targeting to be expressed declaratively.
7. **Audit log volume** — 50 changes/hour is nothing, but change-with-rollback loops can be high; index it and keep it long-lived because it is also a compliance artifact.
8. **The flag store being the source of truth for defaults** — if the store is down you can still serve config from the published artifact, and the SDK's compiled defaults cover a cold start.

---

**9. Failure modes and how the design degrades**

| Failure | Degraded behavior | Invariant |
|---|---|---|
| Flag service fully down | SDKs serve last-known config, then compiled defaults | the request path never calls the service |
| Push channel down | periodic fetch takes over; staleness grows | bounded by `maxStaleMs` |
| Corrupt config artifact | checksum/signature failure → keep last good config | never accept an unverifiable config |
| Buggy flag value (wrong type, missing variant) | evaluation returns the compiled default and logs | evaluation is total, never throws |
| Bad targeting rule | rejected at write time; SDK ignores malformed rules | no evaluation-time failure |
| Kill switch not propagating | synthetic check alerts within seconds | kill switch has its own priority path |
| A flag at 100% rollout starts causing harm | ramp back down; default is the safe state | rollback is not an outage |
| Config too large for a client | client fetches only its namespace; unsupported flags fall back | size caps with alerts |
| Sticky bucketing bug deployed | users flap between variants | checksum + version + monitoring on flip rate |
| Audit log write fails | change rejected — an unlogged change is an unapproved change | audit is on the write path |
| Flag store unavailable | writes blocked; **reads continue from published config** | you cannot safely change flags, but you can keep serving |
| New flag added before clients are ready | older clients get the default via min-version gating | backward compatible |

Invariant: **the flag service is never on the request path.** That single property is what makes all of the above survivable.

---

**10. Trade-offs and rejected alternatives**

| Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Evaluation | local, in-process | remote call per evaluation | 500M/sec is impossible over a network; 1 ms budget |
| Distribution | push + periodic fetch | poll on a short interval | 5 s propagation requirement; polling is 12M requests/instance-group |
| Staleness | bounded, with compiled defaults | strong consistency | availability wins; the bound is documented |
| Rollout | staged with auto-rollback | manual percentage bumps | catches bad rollouts at 1% instead of 100% |
| Kill switch | separate priority override | just another rule | must be < 1 s and independent of targeting |
| Sticky bucketing | deterministic hash of key+seed+userId | random per evaluation | users would flap |
| Storage | relational store + published artifact | KV only | you want audit, ownership, and lifecycle queries |
| Lifecycle | mandatory owner + expiry + auto-retirement | cleanup by convention | flag debt is the actual failure mode |
| Audit | append-only, blocking | best-effort log | an unlogged change is an unapproved change |
| Config | versioned + checksum + min-client-version | unversioned blob | protects old clients from new variants |
| Experimentation | flag service with exposure logging | a separate experimentation platform | avoid duplicating targeting logic; integrate later |

Rejected wholesale
- **Config files in the deploy artifact.** Requires a deploy to change behavior; no targeting, no instant kill.
- **Reading flags from a database in the request path.** Latency and an outage dependency.
- **Percentage rollout with random assignment.** Users flap, and experiment results are noise.
- **Flags without expiry.** Within a year nobody knows what half of them do.
- **A shared library of flag definitions per language without a canonical compiled config.** Three SDKs drift and produce three different behaviors for the same user.

---

**11. Follow-up questions with model answers**

**Q1. "The flag service is down. What happens to production?"**
Model answer: Nothing, and that is the entire design goal. Every service evaluates flags from an in-process cache using a compiled-in configuration, so there is no network call in the request path and no runtime dependency. When the flag service is unreachable, SDKs keep serving the last-known-good config — version and checksum verified — for as long as `maxStaleMs` allows, and after that they fall back to the compiled defaults shipped inside the binary. So the behavior during an outage is: the last state anyone intended, degrading to the safe default, with zero requests failing. What is degraded is *control*, not availability: you cannot change a flag or use a kill switch, which is the real cost and the reason I would treat a flag-service outage as a sev-2 rather than a sev-1. Two things protect that path specifically. First, kill switches ride a separate priority channel so they propagate even when the main push channel is congested, with a synthetic check from several regions that pages if propagation exceeds a few seconds. Second, the whole config is also published as a signed artifact fetchable over a CDN, so a new process starting during an outage gets a correct configuration without the service being reachable at all.

**Q2. "How do you roll out to 10% of users without anyone flapping?"**
Model answer: Sticky deterministic bucketing. The bucket is `hash(flagKey + hashSeed + userId) % 10000`, so a user gets the same variant every time for that flag, for the life of the experiment. Including the flag key is important because otherwise the same users get bucketed into the same segment for every flag, which correlates experiments and produces confounding. Including a stored `hashSeed` means I can deliberately re-randomize — for a rerun or a clean re-test — without changing the key or invalidating the code around it. The rollout itself is staged: 1, then 5, then 25, then 50, then 100, with each stage gated on a metric and a time window, and an automatic rollback if the metric breaches the threshold. The variants must also be backward compatible, so the control group keeps working while a variant is removed, and older clients that do not recognize a new variant get the default rather than a crash. And I would log exposure at assignment time, sampled, because the common experiment bug is logging conversions without logging who was actually exposed, which silently invalidates the result.

**Q3. "We have 4,000 flags. Is that a problem?"**
Model answer: Yes, and usually for three specific reasons. First, evaluation cost: every request evaluates several flags, and the rules per flag grow as targeting accumulates, so 4,000 flags with 20 rules each is a lot of scanning. The fix is pre-compiled flat rule structures and a hard microsecond budget per evaluation, and — more importantly — evaluating fewer flags per request rather than all of them. Second, the risk surface: any flag can change behavior in production, so 4,000 flags is 4,000 things that can break, and nobody can reason about the combination. Third, flag debt: most of the 4,000 are almost certainly dead, and nobody knows which. So the fix is lifecycle, not architecture. Every flag requires an owner and an expiry date at creation, enforced by the API. At expiry the system either deletes it or flips it to its default and files a PR, and that flip must be tested because changing the default changes behavior.
