There should be a weekly report of flags that have been at 100% for more than 14 days, which is the delete-it queue. And two rules people break constantly: never reuse a flag key for a different purpose, because a client that cached the old config will exhibit behavior you cannot reproduce; and never nest two flags over the same behavior, because the interaction matrix is untestable.

**Q4. "How do you make flag changes safe?"**
Model answer: Four gates. First, ownership and review: every change is audited with actor, reason, previous value, and new value, and unlogged changes are rejected rather than best-effort, because a change nobody can account for is a change nobody can roll back with confidence. Second, staged rollout with automatic rollback, which turns a bad change from a 100% incident into a 1% one that reverts itself; the stages are gated on a metric and a time window, not on elapsed time alone. Third, safe-by-default construction: the compiled default is the pre-change behavior, so disabling the flag restores the old world rather than an unspecified one, and variant changes only ever *add* variants so a client holding an older config keeps working. Fourth, instant kill switches on a separate priority channel with sub-second propagation verified synthetically, plus a rehearsed rollback runbook. Beyond that, the guardrail that catches the rest is CI: a linter that fails on an unowned or unexpiring flag, an integration test that flips a flag and asserts behavior, and a config-size check. The principle underneath all four is that a flag is a production change, and it deserves the same review, testing, and rollback discipline as a deploy — which is a framing that is usually what makes teams take this seriously.

**Q5. "How do you handle 100 million mobile clients?"**
Model answer: I stop treating them as subscribers. Holding a socket per client is not viable, so mobile uses two mechanisms. First, periodic config fetch from a CDN-hosted, versioned, checksummed artifact — one HTTP request with a strong ETag, so an unchanged config costs a 304 and a few hundred bytes. That gives propagation in the interval rather than instantly, which is fine for feature flags and not fine for kill switches. Second, for anything that must be immediate, a push notification carrying the new config version, so the app updates on the next foreground rather than at the next poll. And third, namespacing: a mobile binary should only receive the flag subset it uses, because shipping 4,000 flags to a phone is a startup cost and a battery cost on every launch. The SDKs also ship the compiled defaults inside the binary, which means a first launch with no network is still correct — a genuinely important property, because a large fraction of mobile sessions start offline. Everything else follows from the same rule: the client is a subscriber to a small artifact, not a caller into a service.

**Q6. "How do you debug which flags are on for this user?"**
Model answer: With the evaluation reason, not with log spelunking. Every evaluation returns `reason` — which rule matched, or `default`, or `kill_switch`, or `version_fallback` — plus the flag version and whether it came from cache or a fetch. That means the support flow is: take the user's id, ask the flag service to evaluate the whole flag set for that user with a reproduction context, and get back the exact value and reason for each flag. That service is deliberately allowed to be slower and to read the authoritative config, because it is not on the request path. I also store a short history of which flags were active for a user (or at least for sampled or flagged users) so that "it was on for me yesterday and off today" is answerable, since flag changes are exactly the kind of thing that makes a bug unreproducible after the fact. And the exposure log doubles as an audit trail for experiments. The failure mode I would guard hardest against is a flag whose value depends on something unlogged — a hostname, a process-local variable, the current minute — because that is a flag that behaves differently in production than in any reproduction attempt.

---

# PHASE 12 DESIGN REVIEW SCENARIOS (12)

Twelve realistic design-review situations. Each has a situation, what a weak answer sounds like, and what a strong answer contains. Rehearse these out loud.

---

**Scenario 1 — "Your URL shortener redirects 1M times a second. Where does it break first?"**

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

Weak: "We'd shard the database and add more cache."
Strong: The hot key comes first — one viral link is 40% of traffic on a single key, so the counter behind it saturates a Redis shard long before any server is overloaded. Answer: promote the hot key into every edge cache, shard the counter 64 ways, or move it to per-process counters pushed every second. Second: egress, at 1M × 250 B = 250 MB/s, which hits the bill before the CPU graph does anything. Third: cache hit rate erosion as the keyspace outgrows memory, which pushes DB QPS from 5k to 50k. The state you want to reach unprompted: the design fails *open* for reads and *closed* for writes.

---

**Scenario 2 — "An engineer says our pastebin will need Cassandra because pastes get huge."**

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

Weak: Agreeing, because blobs are big.
Strong: Ask what "huge" means. 99% of pastes are under 4 KB and live in a Postgres `inline_content` column; the 1% tail is what drives volume, so storage sizing from the mean is wrong by roughly 50%. The reasons to move bytes out of a relational store are not capacity — they are heap pressure on large rows, backup/restore time, WAL amplification causing replica lag, and the fact that blobs never fit in a buffer pool while metadata does. But going S3-only costs a two-phase failure mode (metadata exists, object does not) and a second dependency in the create path. So: hybrid, inline under 4 KB, S3 above, and S3 lifecycle for expiry. Mention that a 1 MB response never returns bytes inline — it returns a signed URL.

---

**Scenario 3 — "The rate limiter is our only hard dependency for signup. Fail open or closed?"**

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Weak: "Fail open, always."
Strong: Never a global answer. Classify endpoints: abuse-sensitive (signup, login, OTP, coupon, SMS) fail *closed*; cost-sensitive (inference, paid APIs) fall back to a static budget; ordinary reads fail open. And "fail closed" should not mean reject everything — each instance already holds a 5% local bucket, so on store failure that bucket becomes the sole limiter and the cluster still admits roughly the global limit. One hard rule: never fail closed on an *unknown policy*, because a config bug becomes an outage. Ship limits in shadow mode first; two weeks of "would have throttled" data beats guessing the number.

---

**Scenario 4 — "The interviewer's timeline is 'hot' — one account with 200M followers just posted."**

⚫ Expert · **Deep dive**

Weak: "Add more shards to the fanout."
Strong: Sharding does not help because the write count is the follower count. The correct answer is the hybrid: push-fanned-out above ~100k followers, pull-on-read below, with the pull path reading `recent:{authorId}` as the correctness net so a missed push is not a missed tweet. Rate-limit pushes per follower cohort, skip inactive users, keep an emergency per-account switch, and reconcile published-against-fanned-out so a partial fanout is topped up. Also: replies are never push-fanned-out (that gives anyone a 300M-reach spam vector) and retweets are resolved at read time rather than materialized, because retweet fanout makes a single tweet's reach unbounded. Rank and fan out at conversation granularity, render at tweet granularity.

---

**Scenario 5 — "Payments: the capture timed out. Should we retry?"**

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Weak: "Retry, it's idempotent at the provider."
Strong: Only with the *same* idempotency key. Retrying with a fresh key is precisely how a double charge happens. Correct sequence: persist the attempt before calling the provider, return `202 pending` so the merchant does not retry on a 5xx, propagate the same key on any retry so the provider collapses the duplicate, and let daily reconciliation against the settlement file determine the real outcome. Also make the ordering right in the first place: authorize → persist the order → capture, so the worst case is a void rather than money with no order. Mention the provider-native idempotency key as the single most important integration detail.

---

**Scenario 6 — "Review: 'we'll just add Redis in front of the database.'"**

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Weak: Approving it as a plan.
Strong: Three required questions. What is the staleness bound per data class, and which data may *not* be cached at all (permissions, money, inventory)? What happens on eviction — does anything become the only copy? What happens when the working set exceeds memory and the hit rate collapses, sending a 20× read spike to an origin sized for 95% hit rate? Those three answers are the design. Add: per-namespace quotas so one noisy namespace cannot evict another's keys, cardinality caps per tenant, a hit-rate circuit breaker that bypasses the cache to protect the origin, and a warm-up job after every restart.

---

**Scenario 7 — "The seat map shows seat B-7 as available but checkout says it is taken."**

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

Weak: "Increase cache consistency."
Strong: This is by design and the correct boundary is *seat-map reads are eventually consistent, seat claims are strongly consistent*. A 2–5 second stale map is acceptable; a double sale is not. So: read from a shared cache with per-viewer holds merged on top, invalidate targeted via pub/sub on state change, and accept the conflict at hold time with an explicit `conflicts[]` list so a multi-seat booking never silently drops a seat. The partial unique index on `(show_id, seat_id) WHERE state='confirmed'` is what makes the sale itself safe. Also: seat maps are the same bytes for every viewer, so one cache read plus a small per-user overlay scales to millions of viewers.

---

**Scenario 8 — "Your crawler is banned from a large site. What did you do wrong?"**

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Weak: "We did nothing wrong, robots.txt said we could."
Strong: Start by checking robots.txt handling, crawl-delay, and whether `Retry-After` was honored — ignoring `Retry-After` is the single most common way crawlers get banned at scale. Then check per-host concurrency: politeness must be enforced by a shared per-host token bucket, not by each worker sleeping, or 100 workers collectively exceed the limit. Then robots.txt unreachable-with-5xx handling, which must be treated as disallow-all per RFC 9309. Then crawl-trap protection: aggressive canonicalization, per-directory URL budgets, and tracking-parameter stripping. Then conditional GET, which makes a "no change" fetch cost ~200 B instead of 20 KB. Finally, adaptive frequency per host for slow responders.

---

**Scenario 9 — "Two people are typing in the same document and one's characters keep disappearing."**

⚫ Expert · **Deep dive**

Weak: "Add a mutex around the document."
Strong: That destroys concurrency and still loses work across regions. The answer is a concurrency-control algorithm: OT with a server as the ordering point, or a CRDT where operations commute. Then pivot immediately to the systems problem, which is what the interviewer is actually testing: the client renders locally in under 50 ms and queues ops durably before sending; the server appends to a per-document ordered log and acks only after the append is durable; reconnect replays idempotently by `clientId` + `seq`; snapshots compact the log and a restore creates a *new* revision rather than rewinding. Mention per-user transform-aware undo as the subtlest bug, and randomized multi-client testing as the only way these bugs get found.

---

**Scenario 10 — "Our p99 is 400 ms but p50 is 8 ms. Where do you look?"**

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Weak: "Add more instances."
Strong: A 50× p50/p99 gap is a tail problem, so more instances will not help. Check, in order: garbage collection pauses and lock contention (a shared cache client across threads, an unbuffered logger), connection-pool starvation (too few connections shows up as client-side queuing that looks exactly like backend latency), cross-AZ calls where the primary DB is in one AZ and the service spans three, tail-heavy dependencies like a cold cache or a remote region read, and a few slow shards behind a fan-out where the p50 hides a bad quartile. Also: a small number of very large requests (a 50 MB upload on the same endpoint) drags p99 without affecting p50. The tell is that adding capacity did not move p99 at all.

---

**Scenario 11 — "Your push notification system is your highest user-uninstall driver."**

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Weak: "Send fewer notifications."
Strong: Send the *right* ones. Three layers, in order: user preferences first (explicit and authoritative), grouping second (a deterministic `group_key` collapses 5M likes into one push), per-user per-channel caps third (token bucket per user per channel — push 10/hour, email 5/hour, SMS 3/hour). The cap drops the lowest-priority notification, and a mention never loses to a like. Then the loop: cancellations and churn are driven by volume, not by relevance, so the metric to watch is per-*recipient* block/report rate per sender, which catches coordinated spam rings that stay under every per-account limit. Also: the system must be strictly decoupled from the action that generated it, so a notification failure never fails a comment.

---

**Scenario 12 — "The interviewer hands you a whiteboard and says 'design Twitter in 45 minutes.'"**

⚫ Expert · **Deep dive**

Weak: Starting at the boxes and never doing arithmetic.
Strong: Spend the first 5 minutes on arithmetic and say the numbers out loud, because that is what separates a design from a sketch. Fan-out-on-write is 3 billion writes/sec if you materialize every follow; fan-out-on-read is 100 million candidate materializations/sec at read scale; therefore hybrid, split by content class. Then box it: clients → timeline service → merge pushed ZSET plus pulled `recent:` sets → filter blocks/mutes/kill-set → hydrate → paginate by `(score, tweetId)` cursor. Then ranking as a *batch* job writing scores into the ZSET, with an on-write provisional score for freshness. Then name the three deep dives you would go to next: hybrid fanout, replies/retweets/conversations, and removal propagation. Stating "these three are where the hard parts are" is worth more than drawing a fourth box.

---

# PHASE 12 INTERVIEW Q&A (40 questions with model answers)

---

### Estimation and capacity (1–8)

**1. You estimate every system design problem. What are the seven numbers you always produce?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — QPS (write and read, separately), storage volume and growth per year, bandwidth (egress specifically), memory, availability budget in minutes per month, and the read:write ratio. Say the ratio out loud; it decides your architecture more often than the absolute numbers.

**2. What is the fastest way to sound senior in estimation?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — Round deliberately, state assumptions, and sanity-check against a known anchor ("about 1M concurrent streams is the scale Netflix describes"). Then say the number that actually matters and why. A wrong number stated confidently is worse than a right number with a stated assumption.

**3. How do you estimate without knowing the user count?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — Work backwards from the business constraint you *do* have: a funded company, a target city count, a known industry conversion rate, or the traffic of an analogous product. Then be explicit that every number downstream is a function of that assumption.

**4. When does egress dominate storage?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — When reads vastly exceed writes or the objects are large relative to the corpus. Video is the extreme case: a 450 TB corpus with 12 PB/day of egress. The test is `bytes_out_per_day × days_of_retention × price` versus the corpus size; if the first is two orders of magnitude larger, egress is the design constraint.

**5. How do you size a cache?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — As a percentage of the working set: the top 10–20% of keys/objects by access frequency, with 30% memory headroom. Then check the fit twice: process overhead for small values is 30–50%, and eviction at 100% is a cliff, not a slope.

**6. Why is a read:write ratio of 1000:1 a design decision?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — It means reads are cheap relative to writes, so denormalize and cache aggressively on the read side; write consistency can usually be relaxed in exchange for read latency. It also means the write path can afford to do work that pre-computes for readers.

**7. How do you estimate a hot-key workload?**
⚫ Expert · **Deep dive** — Zipfian skew dominates. Ask for the follower/viewer distribution rather than the average: mean is useless, top-1% share is the number. Then design for the tail explicitly with L1 promotion, counter replication, or sharded keys.

**8. What is the availability budget for a 99.9% service in minutes?**
🟢 Beginner · **Nice-to-know** — 43.8 minutes/month. For 99.99% it is 4.3 minutes; for 99.95%, 21.6. Converting it to a real number forces you to reason about what you can and cannot fail open.

---

### Data modeling and storage (9–16)

**9. When do you use a document store over relational?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — Heterogeneous attributes, read-whole access patterns, slow writes with hot reads, and no need for cross-document joins or transactional integrity across them. Products, feature-flag rules, and user profiles qualify; orders and payments do not.

**10. How do you model a schema-less entity in Postgres?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — `JSONB` with a GIN index for containment queries plus extracted, indexed columns for the fields you actually filter and sort on. Say that the fields you query become real columns, which removes most of the argument for a document store.

**11. Why shard by `parent_id` in a file tree?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — It concentrates a folder's children in one partition, so recursive listing and folder-level changes are local, and a single hot folder is the only hot partition you have to think about. The cost is that a 50k-member shared folder is one shard, which needs a different key.

**12. What does `ORDER BY` cost at 100 billion rows?**
⚫ Expert · **Deep dive** — With no supporting index, a full sort of the entire table; with an index it is an index scan, which still means billions of row lookups. Neither is acceptable in a request path. The answer is a precomputed materialized structure, keyset pagination, or a range-partitioned store with a covering index.

**13. When do you denormalize, and how do you undo it?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — When a read needs a join on a hot path, denormalize into the read model and rebuild it from events or a batch job. Undo means the denormalized copy is always derived and rebuildable, never authoritative — so the source of truth and the rebuild path both exist before you ship.

**14. Why store timestamps as integers (epoch millis)?**
🟢 Beginner · **Nice-to-know** — Cheaper, no timezone ambiguity, trivially comparable and sortable, and no driver-specific timestamp parsing. Keep timezone as a separate explicit field for display, and use a monotonic clock for durations, not wall time.

**15. What is a partial index and why does it matter for seat booking?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — An index over a subset of rows filtered by a predicate. `UNIQUE (show_id, seat_id) WHERE state='confirmed'` is the whole no-double-booking guarantee: the database refuses the second confirmed sale regardless of any application bug. It also keeps the index small because it only contains live rows.

**16. How do you store time-series data?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — Columnar, time-partitioned, with rollups. Raw at high resolution for a short window, downsampled progressively. Time-series databases exist because the write pattern (append, no updates) and the query pattern (time range, aggregation) do not fit a row store.

---

### Consistency, transactions, and failure (17–26)

**17. Explain ACID vs BASE in one sentence each.**
🟢 Beginner · **Nice-to-know** — ACID: all-or-nothing, consistent, isolated, durable, one transaction across participants. BASE: basically available, soft state, eventually consistent — you get availability and partition tolerance by giving up simultaneous consistency.

**18. What is a saga and when do you use one?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — A distributed transaction as a sequence of local transactions, each with a compensating action, plus a coordinator that triggers compensations on failure. Use it whenever a step is outside your database — a payment provider, a shipping system, an email send. The hard part is writing compensations that are themselves idempotent.

**19. Idempotency: what makes it work?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — A client-supplied key, a unique constraint in your store, a stored response snapshot to replay, and rejection if the same key arrives with a different payload fingerprint. Client-generated alone is insufficient; database-enforced is what makes it correct.

**20. What is the two-generals problem in practice?**
⚫ Expert · **Deep dive** — You cannot reach agreement in an asynchronous system without a third party, so any coordinator design has a window where it does not know whether a message arrived. Practically this is why outbox patterns, reconciliation jobs, and idempotent retries exist: they do not solve the problem, they make the ambiguity survivable.

**21. How do you handle a message processed twice?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — Assume at-least-once and make the handler idempotent: deduplicate by event id, make writes conditional, or make the operation naturally idempotent (setting a value rather than incrementing). Never rely on exactly-once delivery.

**22. What is the difference between at-least-once and exactly-once?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — At-least-once means duplicates are possible and you dedupe. Exactly-once end-to-end requires both the producer and consumer to participate in the same transaction, which is impossible across independent systems. Where it *is* achievable is a single Kafka transaction plus a consumer-side transactional write to the same store.

**23. When do you fail open and when do you fail closed?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — Fail closed when continuing is unsafe or expensive (auth, payment, abuse prevention, capacity protection). Fail open when availability matters more than the control (reads, recommendations, analytics). The subtlety is that it is a *per-endpoint* decision, not a per-service one.

**24. What is a poison pill message?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — A message that can never be processed successfully and therefore blocks a partition forever in a consumer group. Handle with a retry limit plus a dead-letter queue, and an explicit alert on DLQ depth. Never let unbounded retries sit in the main path.

**25. How do you make a cache correct?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — You do not; you choose a staleness bound per data class. Cache-aside with a TTL is a complete answer for most data, explicit invalidation improves the bound, and version tokens prevent stale messages from resurrecting stale values. For data you cannot tolerate staleness in, do not cache-and-trust.

**26. What is backpressure and where do you implement it?**
⚫ Expert · **Deep dive** — A signal from a saturated consumer to a producer to slow down. Implemented with bounded queues, rate limits, load shedding, and a priority queue per class. In a request path it means capping outbound queues and shedding by priority; in a pipeline it means bounded local buffers that drop rather than block.

---

### Scaling, caching, and data structures (27–36)

**27. Load balancer algorithms, and when do you pick each?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — Round robin: even distribution, no state. Least connections: uneven request costs. Consistent hashing: cache affinity. IP hash: session stickiness but imbalanced on NAT. Power of two choices: best general distribution with O(1) state. Weighted variants for mixed fleets. Choose based on whether you need affinity more than balance.

**28. What is consistent hashing and why virtual nodes?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — Maps keys to a small number of cache nodes so adding a node remaps only ~1/N of keys instead of everything. Virtual nodes give a much more even distribution with few physical nodes and make rebalancing incremental.

**29. Explain bloom filters, including the false positive rate.**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — A probabilistic set using k hashes over m bits; no false negatives, small false-positive rate. Sizing: `m = -n ln(p) / (ln 2)^2`. For 100B URLs at `p = 1e-7`, that is ~1.2 TB — still worth it to avoid a database round trip per discovered link. Use them for existence checks, not for correctness.

**30. What is a skip list and when would you use one?**
🔴 Expert · **Deep dive** — A probabilistic ordered structure with O(log n) search, insert, and range queries. Redis uses it for sorted sets, which is why `ZRANGEBYSCORE` and `ZADD` are O(log n). Useful when you need ordered range queries with concurrent writes and no fixed schema.

**31. Explain the trade-off in a B-tree versus a hash table.**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — B-tree: ordered, O(log n), supports range scans and prefix queries, better with large values on disk. Hash: O(1) point lookups, no ordering, no range scans. Use a B-tree when you need ordering or ranges, a hash when you only do exact lookups.

**32. How does a write-ahead log work?**
🔴 Expert · **Deep dive** — Append the intended change to a log, fsync, then apply it to the data pages. Commit is the fsync, so a crash replays the log to a consistent state. The log is also the replication stream, which is why it is the single most important file in the system.

**33. What is CRDT vs OT for collaborative editing?**
⚫ Expert · **Deep dive** — CRDT makes operations commute, so offline merges need no central ordering; cost is metadata and tombstone GC. OT uses a central server to order operations and transforms each against the ones it missed; cost is a correct transform function per operation pair. Both still need the same systems: ordered per-document log, ack-after-durable, snapshots.

**34. How do you reduce p99 in a fan-out service?**
⚫ Expert · **Deep dive** — Hedge slow calls, parallelize independent work, add a per-dependency timeout shorter than the caller's budget, cache aggressively including negative results, avoid queueing behind a lock, and fail a non-critical dependency rather than propagate its latency. The pattern is: bounded work in the request path, everything else precomputed.

**35. When do you need a message queue versus a stream?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — A queue decouples producers from consumers with per-consumer delivery and work distribution. A stream retains an ordered log that multiple independent consumers read at their own offsets. You need a stream when multiple consumers need the same data; a queue when work should be consumed once.

**36. How do you handle a hot partition in a database?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — Shard by a different key, split a counter into N buckets and sum on read, move the counter off the request path entirely (per-process counters pushed periodically), or use a cache with a hot-key promotion strategy. If the hot key is inherently sequential (a chat conversation, a show's seat map), the answer is to accept the serialization and parallelize only the work around it.

---

### APIs, security, and operations (37–40)

**37. REST versus gRPC versus GraphQL — when?**
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — REST for public, cacheable, resource-oriented APIs and for clients you do not control. gRPC for internal service-to-service, with streaming and strong typing. GraphQL when clients need to shape their own response or the data graph is highly interconnected. Most mature systems use more than one deliberately.

**38. What is the most important thing in an API error response?**
🟢 Beginner · **Nice-to-know** — A stable, machine-readable error code plus enough context to act on: which field, which item, what to do next. For batch operations, which specific items conflicted. "Something went wrong" is the failure mode; returning `{"error":"seats_unavailable","conflicts":["A-2"]}` lets the client render the actual problem.

**39. What security controls belong in a system design for a payments or PII system?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — Tokenization so raw PII never enters your systems, field-level encryption with KMS-managed keys and rotation, least-privilege IAM with no long-lived credentials, full audit of sensitive-data access, PII redaction at the emitter, and a documented retention and deletion policy. Each is a schema decision made before launch, not a control added during an incident.

**40. What do you do in the first 10 minutes of an incident?**
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — Establish scope and blast radius, check whether a recent deploy or config change caused it, and check whether user-facing SLOs are burning. Then decide between rollback and mitigation, preferring the fastest reversible action. Explicitly declare an incident commander and start a timeline. What you deliberately do *not* do is debug before mitigating — diagnose after the bleeding stops.

---

# PHASE 12 RAPID-FIRE (60)

One-line answers. Drill until each is under five seconds.

**1.** QPS read/write ratio decides whether you denormalize and cache.
**2.** 99.9% = 43.8 minutes/month of allowed downtime.
**3.** 99.99% = 4.3 minutes/month.
**4.** Push a product page's immutable half for hours, its price fragment for seconds.
**5.** Availability budget converts "highly available" into a number.
**6.** A hot key concentrates before the average shows anything.
**7.** Zipfian skew is the default shape of real key distributions.
**8.** Sequence numbers over timestamps: device clocks are wrong.
**9.** Idempotency keys prevent duplicate charges, orders, and messages.
**10.** Acknowledge only after a durable append.
**11.** Outbox pattern: never publish in the same transaction as the DB write... actually *do* write the outbox row in the transaction, publish from it.
**12.** Two-phase commit is not available across a payment provider.
**13.** Saga: local transactions plus idempotent compensations.
**14.** DLQ + retry limits, never unbounded retries in the hot path.
**15.** At-least-once delivery + idempotent handlers is the real design.
**16.** Partial unique index = the database enforcing your invariant.
**17.** UUIDv7 / snowflake: time-ordered, no coordination service.
**18.** Sequential IDs make "unlisted" meaningless (Pastebin).
**19.** Random keys prevent enumeration; sequential keys leak volume.
**20.** Bloom filter for existence checks, never for correctness.
**21.** Consistent hashing keeps remapping at 1/N when a node joins.
**22.** `hash(key + seed)` gives sticky percentage bucketing.
**23.** Shard the counter 64 ways to fix a hot key.
**24.** Cache stampede: single-flight plus probabilistic early refresh.
**25.** XFetch threshold ≈ how long the fetch will take.
**26.** TTL jitter prevents a synchronized expiry storm.
**27.** Negative caching stops a 404 storm reaching the origin.
**28.** Eviction policy is LFU/SLRU, not LRU, for scan resistance.
**29.** Per-namespace quotas stop one tenant evicting another's keys.
**30.** Hit-rate circuit breaker protects the origin from the cache.
**31.** Registry + service discovery: client-side load balancing beats a broker hop.
**32.** Circuit breakers for dependencies that fail by class, not by count.
**33.** Bulkheads: thread pools per dependency, not one shared pool.
**34.** Backpressure is a bounded queue plus shedding, not blocking.
**35.** Load shedding by priority beats rejecting everything.
**36.** Canary + automatic rollback on a health-metric threshold.
**37.** Blue/green for correctness, canary for speed.
**38.** Feature flags separate deploy from release.
**39.** Flags need owners and expiry dates or they become debt.
**40.** Kill switches ride a separate priority channel.
**41.** Never let a feature-flag evaluation make a network call.
**42.** Shadow mode before enforcement: log the decision, return success.
**43.** Rate limits: token bucket for bursts, GCRA for smooth shape.
**44.** Fail open on ordinary reads, fail closed on signup and payments.
**45.** A 429 needs a precise `Retry-After` and client-side full jitter.
**46.** Push vs pull fanout: 3B writes/sec vs 100M materializations/sec.
**47.** Rank and fan out at conversation granularity.
**48.** Re-rank periodically in batch; do not rank in the request path.
**49.** On-write provisional score keeps freshness while batching.
**50.** Cursor = `(score, id)` plus the ranking version.
**51.** Prefix any mutable partition key with a tenant or user hash.
**52.** Fan out writes to independent sinks; never chain them serially.
**53.** Cache the query result, not just the database rows.
**54.** `IN` with a large list becomes a slow sequential scan; batch it.
**55.** Deep pagination is a `WHERE key < last` problem, never `OFFSET`.
**56.** Trends want a time-series store; logs want a search store.
**57.** Cardinality, not volume, is what kills a metrics pipeline.
**58.** Tail-sample traces and always keep errors.
**59.** Log retention is the cost story; sample successes, never errors.
**60.** Redact PII at the emitter, because the store has broader access.

---

# PHASE 12 FINAL READINESS CHECKLIST

Work top to bottom. Anything unchecked is a gap to close before you interview.

**Foundations**
- [ ] Can state the CAP trade-off and where availability-first wins
- [ ] Can define idempotency and explain when it is impossible
- [ ] Can distinguish at-least-once, at-most-once, and exactly-once in practice
- [ ] Can sketch an outbox pattern from memory
- [ ] Can write a saga with compensations for a 4-step flow
- [ ] Understand ACID vs BASE, isolation levels, and lost updates

**Estimation**
- [ ] Produce QPS, storage, bandwidth, and availability in under 5 minutes
- [ ] Always separate read QPS from write QPS and state the ratio
- [ ] Always compute egress separately from storage
- [ ] Can size a hot-key workload from a distribution, not a mean
- [ ] Can convert 99.9% into minutes per month and use it

**Data modeling**
- [ ] Comfortable with sharding keys, composite keys, and hot partitions
- [ ] Know when to use a document store, when relational, when both
- [ ] Can justify keyset pagination and say why offset fails
- [ ] Understand partial indexes and can use one as a correctness guarantee
- [ ] Can model a time-series or event-log workload

**Caching and latency**
- [ ] Can name the three algorithm families for rate limiting and their trade-offs
- [ ] Can list five ways to fix a cache stampede
- [ ] Can explain eviction policy choice and memory overhead
- [ ] Understand cache invalidation failure modes and versioning
- [ ] Can identify a p50/p99 gap and its likely causes

**Data structures and algorithms**
- [ ] Hash tables, B-trees, heaps, tries/FSTs, bloom filters
- [ ] Consistent hashing and virtual nodes
- [ ] BFS/DFS/topological sort for dependency and pipeline questions
- [ ] Basic CRDT/OT awareness, and where to point the conversation next
- [ ] Skip list and ordered-set semantics

**Reliability**
- [ ] Can write a full failure-mode table for any system you design
- [ ] Know the degradation ladder pattern and when to apply it
- [ ] Can describe a graceful-degradation policy per component
- [ ] Understand backpressure, load shedding, and bulkheads
- [ ] Can talk about canary rollout, rollback, and shadow mode
- [ ] Understand DLQ, retry budgets, and poison pills

**Scale and operations**
- [ ] Have a mental model of what breaks first in each of the 20 systems
- [ ] Can size queues, connection pools, and thread pools
- [ ] Understand observability: metrics vs logs vs traces, and cardinality
- [ ] Can describe incident response: scope, rollback, timeline
- [ ] Can name the cost driver of each system (egress, storage, GPU, DB)

**Communication**
- [ ] Spend the first 5 minutes on arithmetic, out loud
- [ ] Whiteboard components *and* the request path, with arrows
- [ ] Name three deep dives and let the interviewer choose
- [ ] State trade-offs explicitly, including what you rejected and why
- [ ] Use real numbers; never say "some millions" without a calculation
- [ ] Say "I would start with X and add Y when Z" rather than designing for scale you do not have

**Weak spots to shore up before any loop**
- [ ] Weak: [__________]
- [ ] Weak: [__________]
- [ ] Weak: [__________]

---

# PHASE 12 COMPLETE

You have worked through twenty canonical system designs end to end — the question as it is actually asked, separated requirements, real arithmetic, API contracts, data models with named keys and indexes, high-level designs precise enough to diagram, deep dives on the hard parts, the scaling limits with numbers, failure modes with a degradation ladder, trade-offs with rejected alternatives, and follow-up probes with model answers.

Then twelve design-review scenarios, forty Q&A items spanning estimation to incident response, sixty rapid-fire facts, and this checklist.

What to do next, in order:
1. Rehearse four systems end to end in 45 minutes each, aloud, on a whiteboard, with the arithmetic spoken.
2. For each, prepare the three deep dives and one rejected alternative with reasons.
3. Run the failure-mode table for two systems you find hardest; this is the part interviewers probe.
4. Fill in the three weak spots above.
5. Practice saying "I would start simpler and scale when X" — it is the most senior-sounding sentence in system design.

# PHASE 12: 🆕 System Design

### 🆕 Scalability Principles (🟡 → 🔴)
- Vertical vs horizontal scaling, Load balancing algorithms, Stateless servers, CDN, Database scaling (replication, partitioning, sharding), Microservices trade-offs, Consistent hashing, Auto-scaling, Backpressure

### 🆕 System Estimation (🟡)
- Back-of-envelope math, Traffic (QPS, peak), Storage, Bandwidth, Latency numbers every engineer should know, Availability ("nines"), Bottleneck identification

### 🆕 Distributed Systems Concepts (⚫)
- CAP/PACELC, Consistency models, Consensus (Raft/Paxos awareness), Leader election, Idempotency, Distributed transactions (2PC, saga), Clock and ordering problems, Quorum, Gossip protocols, Failure handling and retries, Rate limiting at scale

### 🆕 System Design Framework (🟡)
- Requirements (functional/non-functional) → estimation → API → data model → high-level design → deep dive → bottlenecks → trade-offs

### 🆕 Classic Case Studies (🔴 → ⚫)
- URL shortener, Pastebin, Rate limiter, Chat/WhatsApp, News feed, Twitter timeline, Notification system, YouTube/Netflix, Dropbox/Google Drive, Uber, Ticket booking (BookMyShow), E-commerce/Amazon, Payment system, Search autocomplete, Web crawler, Google Docs (collaborative editing), Distributed cache, Metrics/logging system, Feature flag service
